Machine Learning for Inverse Problems and Data Assimilation
Eviatar Bach, Ricardo Baptista, Daniel Sanz-Alonso , Andrew Stuart, University of Reading

Abstract




tablechapter
algorithmchapter

Preface

Aim and Overview

The aim of this book is to demonstrate the potential for
ideas in machine learning to impact on the fields of
inverse problems and data assimilation. The perspective
is one that is primarily aimed at researchers from
inverse problems and/or data assimilation who wish to
see a mathematical presentation of machine learning as it
pertains to their fields. As a by-product, we include a
succinct mathematical treatment of various
fundamental
underpinning topics
in machine learning, and adjacent areas of (computational)
mathematics. The fundamental underpinning material is summarized in
Part III. Part I of the book is concerned with
inverse problems, employing material from Part III;
Part II of the book is concerned with
data assimilation, employing material from Parts I and III.
There are different ways to learn from this book, and consequently
there are competing ways in which the material can naturally be organized,
from a pedagogical perspective. In particular, as presented here, the material
allows application-oriented readers to proceed directly to the use of machine learning methodology, in either (or both) of inverse problems (Part I) and data
assimilation (Part II), dipping into the fundamental material in Part III only as it
arises and is used. Theory-oriented readers may instead prefer to
work through the fundamentals in Part III first and, having absorbed this material,
study the applications of machine learning from Parts I and II. Collectively
we have taught from this book in both ways and both have their merits.

Overarching Concepts and Underpinning Fundamental Ideas

Parts I and II, concerning inverse problems and data assimilation respectively, are organized
to parallel one another. Both start with the introductory Chapters
1
and

6
, framing the fields of inverse problems and data assimilation respectively.
We adopt a Bayesian perspective, deriving optimization approaches from it.
And in both chapters we include a section on what is one of the earliest, and most prevalent,
uses of machine learning in the fields of inverse problems and data assimilation, namely
surrogate modeling.
There are then four overarching concepts that define the parallel organization of material
in Parts I and II. In Chapters
2
and
7
we discuss the use of ideas from

variational Bayes
to approximate probability measures from within tractable, and ideally expressive for the task at hand, subclasses of probability measures. Chapters
3
and
8
study the
machine learning
of prior models
, from data. In Chapters
4
and
9
we focus on
transport
as a unifying concept
for the learning of algorithms in inverse problems and data assimilation respectively.
Chapters
5
and
10
discuss
amortization
, demonstrating how the machine learning
ideas developed previously can be generalized
to learn dependence on the observations that define the inverse problems or data assimilation task; such
algorithms can then be reused on multiple instances of the observed data that define inverse
problems or data assimilation tasks.

The key underpinning fundamental ideas used in this book
are contained in Part III. In Chapter
11
we study metrics,
divergences and scoring rules. The first two provide ways of quantifying closeness of two probability measures;
scoring rules assign a notion of closeness to a probability measure and to a point and,
through averaging, can also be used to construct divergences. These notions of closeness
are used in two primary ways: to measure robustness of probability measures to perturbations in problem specification; and to define objective functions used in machine learning. Chapter
12
is devoted to supervised learning, focusing on the use of neural networks, random features and Gaussian processes to approximate functions from data. Chapter
13
is devoted to the topics of unsupervised learning and
generative modeling; in particular we introduce the use of
transport as an important way of framing, and actioning, many tasks in unsupervised learning and generative modeling. In Chapter
14
we discuss time-series forecasting. Much of this material is
fundamental to new emerging methods for purely data-driven data assimilation.
The ideas in this chapter are important for understanding the current landscape of data assimilation research, but not our focus when studying machine learning for data assimilation in Part II; this is because purely data-driven methods are rapidly evolving and not yet as established as methods that combine model- and data-driven approaches.
Chapter
15
describes optimization and sampling techniques that are broadly useful for defining algorithms in the fields of inverse problems and data assimilation. Optimization also plays a fundamental role in machine learning methods for inverse problems and data assimilation, since it is used for training machine learning models.

This is a rapidly evolving research area and there
are already several
articles that review aspects of the material we will cover. See, for example,
[

]
for uses of ML in inverse problems,
and

for uses of ML in data assimilation.
For background on neural networks and deep learning
see

. For an overview of metrics and divergences, and their
inter-relations, see
[
200
]
; for a recent overview of scoring rules see
[
494
]
.

Prerequisites

Even though the subjects of inverse problems and data assimilation are succinctly reviewed in this book, there remains an assumption of previous knowledge of these topics. The book
[
424
]
(also available in closely related form on arXiv) provides a good background on these topics, and we have tried to maintain similar notation. This book also assumes familiarity with linear algebra, probability, statistics, multivariable calculus, and matrix calculus; we establish notation in these areas in the following subsection. Useful graduate-level references on the topics of linear algebra, probability and statistics include
[
474
,
215
,
501
]
. We also refer the reader to books on inverse problems
[
160
,
266
,
489
]
and data assimilation
[
32
,
409
,
305
]
, that provide additional expositions on these topics from the mathematical and computational perspectives. And lastly
we refer to the texts

[
2
,
270
,
327
,
470
]

which cover inverse problems and data assimilation from more applications-oriented perspectives.

Notation

Throughout the book we adopt the following notational conventions:

•

Sets

\mathbb{N}
denotes the set of positive integers
\{1,2,3,\ldots\}

and

\mathbb{Z}^{+}:=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}=\{0,1,2,3,\ldots\}
denotes the set of non-negative integers.
For
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
with
m\leq n
we denote by
[[m,n]]
the set of all integers in the
closed interval
[m,n].

\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the set of
d
-dimensional real vectors, and
\mathbb{R}^{+}
denotes the non-negative reals.
The set
B(u,\delta)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes
the open ball of radius
\delta
at
u
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
in the Euclidean norm. The symbol
\varnothing
denotes the empty set. We also recall the Hausdorff distance
between two sets
A,B\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}:

h(A,B)=\max\bigl\{\sup_{a\in A}\inf_{b\in B}|a-b|,\,\sup_{b\in B}\inf_{a\in A}|a-b|\bigr\}.

•

Vector Spaces

Given
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we define
u_{-i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
by

u_{-i}:=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{i-1},u_{i+1},\ldots,u_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1};

we make the natural modification if
i\in\{1,d\}.

The symbol
I_{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}

denotes the identity matrix on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and

\mathrm{Id}
denotes the identity mapping. We denote the Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
|\cdot|
, noting
that it is induced by the inner-product
\langle a,b\rangle=a^{\top}b.
We also use
|\cdot|

to denote the induced norm on matrices and
|\cdot|_{F}
to denote the Frobenius norm.
We let
\mathbb{S}^{r-1}:=\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{r}:\,|u|=1\}
denote the unit sphere in
r
-dimensional Euclidean space.

We say that symmetric matrix
A

is positive definite (resp. positive semidefinite) if
\langle u,Au\rangle
is positive (resp. non-negative)
for all
u\neq 0
, sometimes denoting this by
A>0
(resp.
A\geq 0).
We let
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\textrm{sym}}
denote the
subset of symmetric matrices in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\textrm{sym},>}
the subset of positive definite symmetric matrices
and
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\textrm{sym},\geq}
the subset of positive semidefinite symmetric matrices. We use
\prec
(resp.
\preceq
) to denote ordering
between matrices in the cone of positive definite (resp. semidefinite) symmetric matrices.
Likewise we use
\succ
and
\succeq.

We often wish to use covariance-weighted inner-product and norm and, to this end,
for covariance matrix
A>0,
we define
|v|_{A}^{2}=v^{\top}A^{-1}v
.
This norm
|\cdot|_{A}
is induced by the weighted Euclidean inner-product

\langle\cdot\;,\;\cdot\rangle_{A}:=\langle\cdot\;,A^{-1}\cdot\rangle.
We use
A^{+}
to denote the Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse of
A
.

The outer product
\otimes
between two vectors
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is defined by the following identity,
assumed to hold for all vectors
c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:

(a\otimes b)c=\langle b,c\rangle a.
We also use
\otimes
to denote the Kronecker product between matrices.
We use
\det
and
\mathrm{Tr}
to denote the determinant
and trace functions on matrices. The Kronecker delta is
\delta_{ij}=1
for
i=j
and

0
otherwise.

•

Probability
Unless otherwise stated, throughout this book random variables
take values in Euclidean space, here denoted
\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Any such random variable has probability distribution defined by a probability measure, say
\mu
, on
\mathbb{R}^{d}.

We write
u\sim\mu
for random variable distributed as
\mu.

We will mainly consider probability measures
characterized by a
probability density function
, say
\rho
, with respect to Lebesgue measure;
we view
\rho
as an element of
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
which is non-negative Lebesgue-a.e. in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and satisfies

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(u)\,du=1.

Because we mainly work with probability density functions we will sometimes blur the distinction between probability measures and probability density functions. In particular, when a random variable
u
has probability density function
\rho

we will write
u\sim\rho
rather than
u\sim\mu.
We write i.i.d. for independent and identically distributed. We let
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote the space of all probability measures
(or probability density functions) over
\mathbb{R}^{d};
we simply write
\mathcal{P}
if the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is clear.

An
unnormalized density

\tilde{\rho}
is an element of
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
which is non-negative
Lebesgue-a.e. in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and has positive norm in
L^{1}.
Then
\rho
defined by

\displaystyle\rho(u)

\displaystyle=Z^{-1}\widetilde{\rho},

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widetilde{\rho}(v)dv

is a probability density function.

Occasionally we will need to
employ Dirac measures; these are not characterized by a probability
density function in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}).
However we will
use the notational convention that the Dirac measure at point
v

has “density”
\delta(\cdot-v)
, also denoted by
\delta_{v}(\cdot).
A particular use of Dirac
measures arises in the construction of an
empirical measure
, approximating another measure
\mu
:
if
\{v^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}\sim\mu
are i.i.d. then we may form an empirical approximation of
\mu

as
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{v^{(n)}}.

A
centered random variable
is a random variable with mean zero. Similarly, a probability density function
\rho
is called centered if
u\sim\rho
is a centered random variable. Given probability density function
\rho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and function
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
g_{\sharp}\rho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{r})
denotes the probability density function
of the random variable
g(z)
where
z\sim\rho.
We refer to
g_{\sharp}\rho
as
the
pushforward
of
\rho
under
g
.
Formally the pushforward notation
g_{\sharp}\rho
means that
\mathbb{P}^{g_{\sharp}\rho}(A)=\mathbb{P}^{\rho}(g^{-1}(A))
for any Borel set
A
. We note that, in some other texts, the pushforward is denoted by
g\#\rho
or
g_{\star}\rho
; we
do not use either of these notational conventions. We denote the
convolution

of two probability density functions
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
as
\rho*\rho^{\prime}.
Thus, if
u\sim\rho
and
u^{\prime}\sim\rho^{\prime}
are independent random variables, then
u+u^{\prime}\sim\rho*\rho^{\prime}.

We sometimes denote by
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot),\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot\;|\;\cdot)
the probability density function of
a random variable and its
conditional
probability density function, respectively. For jointly varying random variables
(u,v)
we let
u|v
denote the random variable found by conditioning
u
on a specific realization of random variable
v.
Thus
u|v
has probability density function
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(u|v);
and we write
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(u)
and
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v)
for the
marginal

probability densities on
u
and
v
, respectively. If the random variables
u
and
v
are independent then we write
u\perp\!\!\!\!\perp v.

We denote by
F_{\rho}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
the
cumulative distribution function
defined by probability density function
\rho
of a real-valued random variable:

F_{\rho}(u)=\int_{-\infty}^{u}\rho(w)\,dw.

Given
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
we denote by

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{u\sim\rho}f(u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(u)\rho(u)\,du

the expectation of
f
with respect to probability density function
\rho
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}.
On occasion, given function
f
defined over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we may denote this expectation by
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\rho}f
or
\rho(f),
when the meaning is clear. The

characteristic function
of
\rho
is

\varphi_{\rho}(w)=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{u\sim\rho}\exp\bigl(i\langle u,w\rangle\bigr).

We write
\mathcal{N}\bigl(m,\Sigma\bigr)
for the distribution of a Gaussian random variable on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with mean
m
and covariance
\Sigma.

We denote by
\mathcal{N}\bigl(u;m,\Sigma\bigr)
the probability density function
of this random variable evaluated at
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

•

Calculus
Given function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
we denote by
Df:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
the
gradient

and by
D^{2}f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
the
Hessian
.
For more general
functions
f:\cal{V}\to\mathbb{R}
acting on elements
v
of vector space
\cal{V}

we write
Df
to denote the derivative. We also directly consider functions

f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
Jacobian matrix

Df:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}.
When there are two potentially varying arguments and we wish to indicate
differentiation with respect to only one of them, say
v
, we will indicate
this with a subscript:
D_{v}
(and similarly for second derivatives). When we contract a derivative
to obtain
divergence
we write
\mathrm{div}\,
: for example, for
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
{\rm Tr}\,Df=\mathrm{div}\,f.
For
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
we write the (typically time-) derivative as
\frac{df}{dt}.

•

Functions

We use
f\circ g
to indicate the composition of
f
and
g
, assuming input dimensions (of
f
) and output dimensions (of
g
) are compatible, allowing composition. We write
\mathsf{id}
for the identity mapping on
\mathbb{R}^{d}:

\mathsf{id}(u)=u.

Finally if
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
then we define the
indicator function

\mathbb{1}_{A}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
by
\mathbb{1}_{A}(x)=1
for

x\in A
and
\mathbb{1}_{A}(x)=0
otherwise. For a subset
A\subset\mathbb{R}
of cardinality
N
, and a point
x\in\mathbb{R}
, the
rank function

\operatorname{rank}(A,x)\in[[0,N]]
, returns the rank of value
x
in set
A
: the number of points in
A
that are less than or equal to
x
.

Part I
Inverse Problems

Chapter 1
Bayesian Inversion

A forward model
G:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}

specifies output
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
from input

u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by the relationship
y=G(u).
The
related inverse problem is to
recover unknown parameter
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
from
data, or observation,

y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
defined by

(1.1)

y=G(u)+\eta;

here
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
denotes observation noise.
In the Bayesian approach to the inverse problem we view

(u,y)
as jointly varying random variables and the solution of the
inverse problem is the posterior distribution
on parameter
u
, given a specific instance of the
data
y,
found by conditioning the joint distribution.
Implicit in this framing of the inverse problem is that,
although
\eta
is unknown, its distribution is known.

In Section
1.1
we formulate Bayesian inverse problems and state Bayes Theorem, giving an explicit expression for the posterior probability density function. In Section
1.2
we discuss finite-dimensional summaries of the posterior and we connect Bayesian inversion to the classical optimization-based approach to inverse problems.
Section
1.3
describes the well-posedness of the Bayesian formulation, using the Hellinger
distance
from Chapter
11
. In Section
1.5
we discuss
various perspectives on model error. Section
1.6
studies the
use of machine-learned surrogate models, serving as an introduction to the potential for
the use of machine learning within inverse problems. We conclude the chapter in
Section
1.7
, containing bibliographical remarks.
In this chapter we do not cover classical computational methods for solving Bayesian inverse problems.
However we describe both optimization and Markov chain Monte Carlo methods
in Chapter
15
.

1.1
Bayesian Inversion

We view
(u,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
as a random variable, whose joint distribution is specified by means of the identity (
1.1
) and the following assumption on the distribution of
(u,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}:

Assumption 1.1
.

The distribution of the random variable
(u,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
is defined by assuming that
u\perp\!\!\!\!\perp\eta
, that
u\sim\rho
and that
\eta\sim\nu
, where
\rho
and
\nu
are probability density functions and, in addition,
\nu
is centered.

We make the following assumption about the provenance of the data:

Data Assumption 1.2
.

Data
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
is given and is assumed to have come from a realization of the random variable
(u,\eta)
,
under Assumption
1.1
, and identity (
1.1
).

Combining these assumptions we can define the
Bayesian inverse problem, a specific and important
subclass of general Bayesian inference.
We refer to
\rho
, the probability density function of
u
, as the
prior
probability density function. Given
identity (
1.1
), then for fixed
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d},
the distribution of
y
given
u

defines the
likelihood
:

(1.2)

y|u\sim\mathsf{l}(y|u):=\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr).

The
posterior
probability density function is the conditional distribution of
u
given
y
, that is the distribution of random
variable
u|y
. The posterior density is the solution to the Bayesian
formulation of the inverse problem.

The primary computational challenge associated with Bayesian inversion is that the posterior
is an infinite-dimensional object, even when
u
is finite-dimensional.
In particular it is important to appreciate that, although
Bayes Theorem (which follows) delivers a formula
for the probability density function of
u|y
, the task of obtaining
information from this probability density function, for example by drawing many samples from it, is,
in general, a substantial challenge.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let Assumption
1.1
and Data Assumption
1.2
hold,
and assume further that

Z=Z^{y}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr)\rho(u)\,du>0.

Then
u|y\sim\pi^{y},
where
1
1

1

When there is no possibility of confusion,
we will simply write
\pi
for the posterior probability density function, rather than

\pi^{y}.

(1.3)

\pi^{y}(u)=\frac{1}{Z}\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr)\rho(u).

Proof.

Recall from the Preface that we denote by
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot)
the probability density function of a random variable; and we denote
by
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot|\cdot)
the probability density function conditional on the second argument.
The standard laws of conditional probability give the two identities

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(u,y)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(u|y)\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y),\textrm{ if }\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y)>0,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(u,y)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y|u)\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(u),\textrm{ if }\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(u)>0.

The marginal probability density function on
y
is given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(u,y)\,du

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y|u)\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(u)\,du=Z,

and so the assumption that
Z>0
ensures that the data could indeed have been
obtained from the postulated model. Since
Z>0
we have, by combining the
two identities above, the desired result

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(u|y)=\frac{1}{\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y)}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y|u)\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(u)=\frac{1}{\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y)}\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr)\rho(u).

∎

Remark 1.4
.

We note that the
normalization constant

Z
can be written as
an expectation with respect to the prior:

(1.4)

Z=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}[\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr)].

Thus Bayes formula (
1.3
) can be written as

(1.5)

\pi^{y}(u)=\frac{\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr)\rho(u)}{\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}[\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr)]}.

This makes explicit that Bayes formula may be viewed as a nonlinear map
2
2

2

Strictly speaking
{\mathsf{A}}
is nonlinear when viewed as acting on the vector space
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
; the space of probability density functions is a subset of this vector space, but is not a linear subspace.

taking prior
\rho
into posterior
\pi^{y}:

(1.6)

\pi^{y}={\mathsf{A}}(\rho;y).

This notation will be particularly useful when we consider data assimilation
in Chapter
6
. In that specific context map
{\mathsf{A}}
is known as the analysis map.
We highlight that exact form of the map
{\mathsf{A}}
is determined by the likelihood.
∎

Remark 1.5
.

We write the density of the
joint distribution of
(y,u)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
as
\gamma(y,u).

We write the marginal density on
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
as
\kappa(y).
Note that

\kappa(y)=Z.

∎

Remark 1.6
.

If
G
is linear and
(u,\eta)
is Gaussian
then the posterior on
u|y

is Gaussian. This Gaussian setting provides an explicitly solvable
inverse problem in which the posterior is characterized
by its mean and covariance; it
reduces the infinite-dimensional problem to
a finite-dimensional problem for a vector (mean) in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a
positive definite matrix (covariance) in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}.

To see this explicitly, let
G(u)=Au
for matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
, and
assume that
u\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C)
and
\eta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Gamma)
for positive definite covariance matrices
C
and
\Gamma
.
Using independence of
u
and
\eta
to define the likelihood
and completing the square, we see that setting

(1.7a)

\displaystyle C^{-1}

\displaystyle=C^{-1}+A^{\top}\Gamma^{-1}A,

(1.7b)

\displaystyle C^{-1}m

\displaystyle=A^{\top}\Gamma^{-1}y,

we have that
\pi^{{y}}=\mathcal{N}(m,{C}).

∎

Remark 1.7
.

We have concentrated on formulating Bayes Theorem around equation (
1.1
)
in which the noise appears additively and is independent of the unknown
u.

However Bayes Theorem is not restricted to this setting. Section
13.1
provides
an explicit example, going beyond this additive noise setting,
to formulate density estimation in a Bayesian fashion.
∎

We conclude by discussing the following modification of Data Assumption
1.2
:

Data Assumption 1.8
.

Assume that
u^{\mathsf{true}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a deterministic true unknown parameter and that

y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
is given by

(1.8)

y=G(u^{\mathsf{true}})+\eta,

where
\eta
is an unknown realization drawn from given centered noise probability density function
\nu.

Remark 1.9
.

This assumption is useful for two primary reasons. The first is as the basis for analysis of Bayesian methods from the
frequentist
perspective; for instance, it can be important to understand whether the posterior distribution concentrates around true parameter
u^{\mathsf{true}}
if the variance of the noise
\eta
is small or if the volume of data is high, and then to quantify the concentration rate.
A second use of the assumption in the Bayesian context is when studying
model misspecification
; in particular it can be useful when the data
y
is derived from a true unknown
u^{\mathsf{true}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that could not have been drawn from the prior
\rho.

∎

1.2
Finite-Dimensional Summaries

The
posterior
\pi^{y}
contains all knowledge
about the parameter
u
, given Assumption
1.1
and Data Assumption
1.2
.
It is often useful, however, to extract finite-dimensional information from the posterior distribution, in order to summarize aspects of the distribution.
Remark
1.6
identifies a specific situation where
a finite-dimensional summary, comprising the posterior mean and covariance, fully characterizes the posterior, since the posterior is Gaussian. In general, however, we must seek finite-dimensional summaries that will not fully characterize the posterior. These summaries are often computed using optimization and sampling algorithms described in Chapter
15
.

1.2.1
Maximum
a Posteriori
Estimator

A natural summary
is the
posterior mode
or MAP estimator:

Definition 1.10
.

A
maximum
a posteriori
(MAP) estimator
of
u
given data
y
is defined as any point
u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}
satisfying

u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}\in\argmax_{{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\pi^{{y}}({u}).

∎

Optimization algorithms are often required to compute the MAP estimator. We now show how MAP estimation connects with classical optimization-based approaches to inversion.
Recall that the posterior probability density function
\pi^{y}
on
u|y
from Theorem
1.3
has the form

\pi^{y}({u})=\frac{1}{Z}\nu\bigl({y}-{G}({u})\bigr)\rho({u}).

Recalling (
1.2
) we define a
loss function

(1.9)

\mathsf{L}({u})=-\log\nu\bigl({y}-{G}({u})\bigr)=-\log\mathsf{l}(y|u),

and a
regularizer

(1.10)

\mathsf{R}({u})=-\log\rho({u}).

In fact we will refer to any function which differs from
\mathsf{L}({u})

(resp.
\mathsf{R}({u})
) by a constant independent of
u
as the
loss (resp. regularizer).
Adding the loss function and regularizer we obtain
an
objective function
of the form

(1.11)

{\mathsf{J}}({u})=\mathsf{L}({u})+\mathsf{R}({u}).

Furthermore

\pi^{y}({u})=\frac{1}{Z}\nu\bigl({y}-{G}({u})\bigr)\rho({u})\propto e^{-{\mathsf{J}}({u})}.

Thus the MAP estimator can be rewritten as a minimizer
of
{\mathsf{J}}
as follows:

(1.12a)

\displaystyle u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}

\displaystyle\in\argmax_{{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\pi^{{y}}({u})

(1.12b)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathsf{J}}(u);

minimizing the objective function
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)

is equivalent to determining a MAP estimator defined by
maximizing the posterior probability density function
\pi^{y}(\cdot)
.

Remark 1.11
.

The optimization problem defining the MAP estimator does not depend on the
normalization constant
Z
in the expression for the posterior in Bayes Theorem
1.3
. This is computationally significant,
as it eliminates the need to compute an integral over
\mathbb{R}^{d}.

∎

Remark 1.12
.

Setting the regularizer to zero, known as choosing a
flat prior
,
we obtain the maximum likelihood estimation (MLE)
problem.
∎

Remark 1.13
.

The formulation involving minimization of
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)
is simplest
when the argument of the logarithm in (
1.10
), the prior, is strictly positive
on all of
\mathbb{R}^{d}.
However where the prior
\rho
is zero we may interpret the regularizer

\mathsf{R}
to take value
+\infty
. The effect of this is to confine the
minimization problem to the support of the prior.
∎

Example 1.14

(Gaussian Observation Noise and
Gaussian Prior)
.

If
\nu=\mathcal{N}({0},{\Gamma})
, for positive definite covariance
\Gamma
, then

\nu\bigl({y}-{G}({u})\bigr)\propto\exp(-\frac{1}{2}|{y}-{G}({u})|_{{\Gamma}}^{2}).

Thus in this case the loss is

\mathsf{L}({u})=\frac{1}{2}|{y}-{G}({u})|_{{\Gamma}}^{2},

a
{\Gamma}
-weighted
\ell_{2}
loss.
Now assume that the prior is a centered Gaussian

\rho=\mathcal{N}({0},{C})
, where
C
is positive definite.
Then the corresponding regularizer is given by

\mathsf{R}({u})=\frac{1}{2}|{u}|_{{C}}^{2}.

Combining these assumptions we obtain a canonical objective function

(1.13)

{\mathsf{J}}({u})=\frac{1}{2}|{y}-{G}({u})|_{{\Gamma}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|{u}|_{{C}}^{2}.

We refer to minimization of (
1.13
)
as the
Tikhonov–Phillips regularized inverse
problem
.
If
\Gamma=I_{k}
and
C=\lambda^{-1}I_{d}

then this reduces
to the classical
Tikhonov regularized inverse problem

with objective function

(1.14)

{\mathsf{J}}({u})=\frac{1}{2}|{y}-{G}({u})|^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2}|{u}|^{2}.

Finally, consider the setting of Remark
1.6
, where
G(u)=Au
and
the observation noise and prior are Gaussian.
Then the MAP estimator related
to the Tikhonov–Phillips regularized inverse
problem is unique and
given by point
m
defined in (
1.7
).
In this case the posterior is Gaussian
and the posterior mean is equal to the (in this case unique) MAP estimator.
For general non-Gaussian posteriors, however,
the MAP estimator
and the posterior mean do not coincide.
∎

Example 1.15

(
\ell_{1}
Regularizer – Laplace Prior)
.

Now consider the setting where again
\nu=\mathcal{N}({0},{\Gamma})
, but

\rho({u})\propto\exp\biggl(-\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{d}|u_{i}|\biggr)=\exp(-\lambda|{u}|_{1}).

This is known as a Laplace
distribution. Then

\mathsf{R}({u})=\lambda|{u}|_{1}
, an
\ell_{1}
regularizer.
Combining this prior with the weighted
\ell_{2}
loss above,
we obtain the objective function

{\mathsf{J}}({u})=\frac{1}{2}|{y}-{G}({u})|_{{\Gamma}}^{2}+\lambda|{u}|_{1}.

It is well known that regularizers of this type promote sparse solutions when
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)

is minimized. However it is important to appreciate that samples
from the underlying posterior distribution are typically not sparse.
∎

1.2.2
Posterior Expectations

In the previous Subsection
1.2.1
we summarized the posterior distribution
\pi^{y}
using its mode, known as the MAP estimator.
Many other finite-dimensional summaries of the posterior can be obtained through posterior expectations of the form

(1.15)

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\pi^{y}}[f(u)]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(u)\,\pi^{y}(u)\,du,

where
f
is a suitable choice of
test function
, which may take real, vector or matrix values.
Furthermore, using (
1.5
), this can be rewritten as the ratio of two expectations:

(1.16)

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\pi^{y}}[f(u)]=\frac{\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}[\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr)f(u)]}{\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}[\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr)]}.

Both of the preceding formulae can be used as the basis of numerical methods to approximate useful finite-dimensional posterior summaries. Before discussing the numerical methods we first
discuss three important choices of test function and the corresponding posterior summaries.

Example 1.16

(Posterior Mean Estimator)
.

Letting
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the identity map in
\mathbb{R}^{d},
so that
f(u)=u
for all
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d},
we obtain the posterior mean estimator
u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(y):=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\pi^{y}}[u]
of
u
given data
y.
The posterior mean estimator is also sometimes known as the
conditional mean
. In applications such as imaging, the MAP estimator often provides sharper reconstructions than the posterior mean estimator; however, the latter has the advantage of being more stable to perturbations in the model, as will be explained in Section
1.3
below.
∎

An important property of the posterior mean is that it minimizes the squared error in expectation over the posterior, as shown by the following theorem.

Theorem 1.17
.

Assume that
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{u\sim\pi^{y}}[|u|^{2}]<\infty.

For all measurable functions
v(y)
of
y
, we have that

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{u\sim\pi^{y}}\Bigl[|u-u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(y)|^{2}\Bigr]\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{u\sim\pi^{y}}\Bigl[|u-v(y)|^{2}\Bigr];

furthermore, the inequality is strict if
v(y)\neq u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(y)
.

Proof.

We first note that the left- and right- hand side of the inequality are well defined since

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{u\sim\pi^{y}}[|u|^{2}]<\infty.
Straightforward manipulations show that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{u\sim\pi^{y}}\Bigl[|u-v(y)|^{2}\Bigr]=

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{u\sim\pi^{y}}\Bigl[|(u-u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(y))+(u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(y)-v(y))|^{2}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{u\sim\pi^{y}}\Bigl[|u-u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(y)|^{2}\Bigr]+2\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{u\sim\pi^{y}}\Bigl[(u-u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(y))^{\top}(u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(y)-v(y))\Bigr]

\displaystyle\qquad+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{u\sim\pi^{y}}\Bigl[|u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(y)-v(y)|^{2}\Bigr].

Now note that the second term is zero, and the expectation is
redundant in the third term. We therefore find

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{u\sim\pi^{y}}\Bigl[|u-v(y)|^{2}\Bigr]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{u\sim\pi^{y}}\Bigl[|u-u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(y)|^{2}\Bigr]+|u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(y)-v(y)|^{2};

This establishes the desired inequality. Furthermore it demonstrates that
the inequality is strict if
v(y)\neq u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(y)
.
∎

Remark 1.18
.

Summary statistics that minimize a cost function in expectation over the posterior are known as
Bayes estimators
. Theorem
1.17
shows that the posterior mean is the Bayes estimator for the squared error. Other Bayes estimators correspond to different cost functions. For example, the posterior median is the Bayes estimator when using the
L^{1}-
norm on
\mathbb{R}
to define the error (absolute-error objective
function). For vector-valued states
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, use of the component-wise
L^{1}-
norm yields the coordinate-wise posterior medians.
∎

Example 1.19

(Posterior Covariance and Variance)
.

Letting
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the outer product
f(u)=(u-u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}})\otimes(u-u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}),
we obtain the posterior covariance
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\pi^{y}}[(u-u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}})\otimes(u-u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}})].
The trace of the posterior covariance, given by

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\pi^{y}}[|u-u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}|^{2}]
gives the posterior variance, which quantifies the spread of the posterior distribution.
∎

Example 1.20

(Posterior Probabilities)
.

Letting
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be the indicator function of a set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d},
so that
f(u)=\mathbb{1}_{A}(u),
we obtain the posterior probability of
A

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\pi^{y}}[\mathbb{1}_{A}(u)]=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi^{y}}(A)
. In some applications, it is important to understand whether the unknown
u
lies in a given set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
of the parameter space. In others, it is important to find set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with a prescribed probability, in order to build
credible intervals
. Credible intervals play a similar role in Bayesian statistics to confidence intervals in frequentist statistics.
∎

Finding the MAP estimator involves solving an optimization problem; in contrast, finding posterior expectations of the form (
1.15
) involves computing an integral, or integrals, over
\mathbb{R}^{d}.
This task can be challenging in high dimension
d
– constants entering errors resulting from standard quadrature rules typically depend badly on dimension. Monte Carlo methods, and Markov chain
Monte Carlo methods, are often preferred over standard quadrature rules because they have favorable dependence on dimension.

Given
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and probability measure
\mathfrak{p}
,
the idea of Monte Carlo methods is to obtain
N
independent samples
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
from
\mathfrak{p}

and make the approximation

(1.17)

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\mathfrak{p}}[g(u)]\approx\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}g(u^{(n)}).

This can be understood as making the approximation

(1.18a)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\mathfrak{p}}[g(u)]

\displaystyle\approx\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\mathfrak{p}^{N}}[g(u)],

(1.18b)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{N}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{u^{(n)}}.

The error incurred in making this approximation is of size
1/\sqrt{N};
this idea is made precise
in Theorem
11.34
. Furthermore the ideas, and approximation theory, can be extended to
functions
g
taking values in general normed vector spaces.

The Monte Carlo idea can be used to approximate posterior expectations under
\pi^{y}
, as follows.
First consider the expression (
1.16
) for posterior expectation
of
f
and notice that it is the ratio of two expectations under
\rho.
Using the Monte
Carlo approximation (
1.17
) with
\mathfrak{p}=\rho
and with
g(\cdot)=\nu\bigl(y-G(\cdot)\bigr)f(\cdot)

and with
g(\cdot)=\nu\bigl(y-G(\cdot)\bigr)
delivers the desired approximation.
This approach is generally applicable provided that we can obtain independent samples from the prior.

In principle, the same approximation (
1.17
) can be used directly with
\mathfrak{p}=\pi^{y}
in (
1.15
).
In practice obtaining
independent
samples that are
exactly
distributed according to
\pi^{y}
is often harder than for the prior, and indeed typically
not possible. However
Markov chain Monte Carlo
(MCMC), discussed in Section
15.6
, provides a widely applicable approach to obtain
correlated
samples
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
from a Markov chain so that
u^{(n)}
is
approximately
distributed according to the posterior for large
N
and (
1.18
) applies with
\mathfrak{p}=\pi^{y}
and
without the independence assumption on samples.
MCMC sampling algorithms are commonly used to compute posterior expectations in Bayesian inverse problems.

Remark 1.21
.

The pure Monte Carlo approach just outlined, using independent prior samples, can be inefficient.
In particular, whenever
\nu\bigl(y-G(\cdot)\bigr)
and
\rho(\cdot)
are largest in different parts of the space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, estimation
of the normalization constant
Z
, the denominator in (
1.16
), is inefficient.
A key feature of MCMC methods for Bayesian inference
is that they do not require approximation of the normalization constant
Z
,
typically making them more
efficient than Monte Carlo approximation from independent prior samples, despite the correlation of the MCMC samples. Recall, also, that the
MAP estimator does not require evaluation of the normalization constant
Z
– see Remark
1.11
.
∎

1.3
Well-Posedness of Bayesian Inverse Problems

The MAP estimator is an unstable quantity in that a small change in the problem specification, such as the forward model
G
or the data
y,
can lead to a large change in the MAP estimator. This is because the minimizer of a non-convex
objective function may change discontinuously with respect to arbitrarily small changes in the objective function itself.
On the other hand, the Bayesian formulation of the inverse problem
leads to stability in broad generality: small changes in the
problem specification lead to small changes in the posterior density and, consequently, to small changes in posterior expectations of suitable test functions. Here
we prove a representative result of this type.

We consider two different likelihoods

\mathsf{l}(y|u)=\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{l}_{\delta}(y|u)=\nu\bigl(y-G_{\delta}(u)\bigr)

associated with two different forward
models
G(u)
and
G_{\delta}(u).

Assuming the associated Bayesian inverse problems both adopt the same prior

\rho
we obtain two different posteriors of the form

\pi^{y}(u)=\frac{1}{Z}\mathsf{l}(y|u)\rho(u)\quad\text{and}\quad\pi_{\delta}^{y}(u)=\frac{1}{Z_{\delta}}\mathsf{l}_{\delta}(y|u)\rho(u),

where
Z,Z_{\delta}
are the corresponding normalizing constants.
Our aim is to show that if
\mathsf{l}(y|u)
and
\mathsf{l}_{\delta}(y|u)
are close, then
so are the associated posteriors. To this end we make the following assumptions
about the likelihoods, in which we view the data
y
as fixed.

Assumption 1.22
.

The data defined by Assumption
1.1
and Data Assumption
1.2

has positive probability under the resulting joint distribution on
(u,y)
, so that
Z>0.
There exist
\delta^{+}>0
and
K_{1},K_{2}<\infty
such that, for all
\delta\in(0,\delta^{+}],

(i)

|\sqrt{\mathsf{l}(y|u)}-\sqrt{\mathsf{l}_{\delta}(y|u)}|\leq\varphi(u)\delta
, for some
\varphi(u)

satisfying
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\rho}[\varphi^{2}(u)]\leq K_{1}^{2}
;

(ii)

\sup_{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}(|\sqrt{\mathsf{l}(y|u)}|+|\sqrt{\mathsf{l}_{\delta}(y|u)}|)\leq K_{2}.

Recall the Hellinger distance
\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\cdot,\cdot)

from Chapter
11
. The main result of this section is:

Theorem 1.23
.

Under Assumption
1.22
, there exist

\Delta,c>0
such that, for all
\delta\in(0,\Delta)
,

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\pi^{y},\pi_{\delta}^{y})\leq c\delta.

Remark 1.24
.

Before proving it, we make two observations about the implications of Theorem
1.23
:

•

The theorem ensures that expectations of test functions
of
u
, with
u
distributed according
to
\pi^{y}
and
\pi_{\delta}^{y}
respectively,
are order
\delta
apart, provided that sufficient moments of those
test functions are available; this may be shown by use of Lemma
11.3
.

•

The theorem is prototypical of a variety of stability results for
Bayesian inversion. Here we have viewed data
y
as fixed and considered error in the likelihood arising from approximation of the forward model.
In Section
1.6
we establish a similar result
in the context of machine learning approximations of the
Bayesian inverse problem. The forward model is replaced by a surrogate found, for example, from
a neural network approximation; this then changes the likelihood.
It is also possible to estimate changes in the posterior, in the Hellinger metric,
with respect to changes in the data; this, of course, also changes the likelihood.

∎

Proof of Theorem
1.23
.

To prove Theorem
1.23
, we first characterize the stability
of the normalization constants.
We show that, under Assumption
1.22
, there exist

\Delta,c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that, for all
\delta\in(0,\Delta)
,

|Z-Z_{\delta}|\leq c_{1}\delta\quad\text{and}\quad Z,Z_{\delta}>c_{2}.

To see this, noting that
Z=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{l}(y|u)\rho(u)\,du
and

Z_{\delta}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{l}_{\delta}(y|u)\rho(u)\,du
,
we have

\displaystyle|Z-Z_{\delta}|=

\displaystyle\biggl|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigl(\mathsf{l}(y|u)-\mathsf{l}_{\delta}(y|u)\bigr)\rho(u)\,du\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Bigl|\sqrt{\mathsf{l}(y|u)}-\sqrt{\mathsf{l}_{\delta}(y|u)}\Bigr|^{2}\rho(u)\,du\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Bigl|\sqrt{\mathsf{l}(y|u)}+\sqrt{\mathsf{l}_{\delta}(y|u)}\Bigr|^{2}\rho(u)\,du\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\delta^{2}\varphi(u)^{2}\rho(u)\,du\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K_{2}^{2}\rho(u)\,du\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ K_{1}K_{2}\delta,\quad\delta\in(0,\delta^{+}).

The Lipschitz stability result follows by taking
c_{1}=K_{1}K_{2}.

Therefore, for
\delta\leq\Delta\coloneqq\min\{\frac{Z}{2K_{1}K_{2}},\delta^{+}\}
, we have

Z_{\delta}\geq Z-|Z-Z_{\delta}|\geq\frac{1}{2}Z.

Since
Z
is assumed positive we deduce the lower bound on
Z_{\delta}
and take
c_{2}=\frac{1}{2}Z.

We now study the Hellinger distance between the two posteriors, using
the preceding estimate on the Lipschitz stability of the normalization constant. To this
end we break the total error into two contributions, one reflecting
the difference between
Z
and
Z_{\delta}
, and the other the difference between
\mathsf{l}
and
\mathsf{l}_{\delta}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\pi^{y},\pi_{\delta}^{y})=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Bigl\|\sqrt{\pi^{y}}-\sqrt{\pi_{\delta}^{y}}\Bigr\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Bigg\|\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho}{Z}}-\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho}{Z_{\delta}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho}{Z_{\delta}}}-\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}_{\delta}\rho}{Z_{\delta}}}\Bigg\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Bigg\|\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho}{Z}}-\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho}{Z_{\delta}}}\Bigg\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Bigg\|\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho}{Z_{\delta}}}-\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}_{\delta}\rho}{Z_{\delta}}}\Bigg\|_{L^{2}}.

From the stability estimate on the normalization constants we have,
for
\delta\in(0,\Delta)
,

\displaystyle\Bigg\|\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho}{Z}}-\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho}{Z_{\delta}}}\Bigg\|_{L^{2}}=

\displaystyle\ \Bigg|\frac{1}{\sqrt{Z}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{Z_{\delta}}}\Bigg|\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{l}(y|u)\rho(u)\,du\Bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{|Z-Z_{\delta}|}{(\sqrt{Z}+\sqrt{Z_{\delta}})\sqrt{Z_{\delta}}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{c_{1}}{2c_{2}}\delta.

From Assumption
1.22
we have

\Bigg\|\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho}{Z_{\delta}}}-\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}_{\delta}\rho}{Z_{\delta}}}\Bigg\|_{L^{2}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{Z_{\delta}}}\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Bigl|\sqrt{\mathsf{l}(y|u)}-\sqrt{\mathsf{l}_{\delta}(y|u)}\Bigr|^{2}\rho(u)\,du\Bigg)^{1/2}\leq\sqrt{\frac{K_{1}^{2}}{c_{2}}}\delta.

Therefore

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\pi^{y},\pi_{\delta}^{y})\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{c_{1}}{2c_{2}}\delta+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{\frac{K_{1}^{2}}{c_{2}}}\delta=c\delta,

with
c=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{c_{1}}{2c_{2}}+\frac{K_{1}}{\sqrt{2c_{2}}}
,
which is independent of
\delta
.
∎

The following corollary of Theorem
1.23
is a straightforward consequence of Lemma
11.2
:

Corollary 1.25

(Well-Posedness of Posterior)
.

Under Assumption
1.22
there exist
\Delta,c>0

such that, for all
\delta\in(0,\Delta)
,

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\pi^{y},\pi_{\delta}^{y})\leq c\delta.

1.4
Evaluating Approximate Posteriors

We now describe a method referred to as
simulation-based calibration
for evaluating approximate posterior distributions. These approximations may be obtained using Monte Carlo, Markov chain Monte Carlo, or by use of the machine-learning based methods described in subsequent chapters. The following theorem, true of the exact posterior distribution, motivates the methodology:

Theorem 1.26
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a scalar function. Given
(u,y)\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(u,y)
and
N
samples
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
drawn i.i.d. from
\pi^{y}
, then
\operatorname{rank}(\{f(u^{(n)})\}_{n=1}^{N},f(u))
is uniformly distributed over integers
[[0,N]]
.

Proof.

The reference for the proof is provided in the bibliography Section
1.7
.
∎

Given this property of the exact posterior, we make the following Data Assumption in order to evaluate an approximate posterior.

Data Assumption 1.27
.

Assume that we can sample
u\sim\rho
and evaluate the forward model
G(u)
, such that we can obtain
L
joint samples

\{(u^{(\ell)},y^{(\ell)})\}_{\ell=1}^{L},

where
(u^{(\ell)},y^{(\ell)})\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(u,y)
.

Then, for each
y^{(\ell)}
, assume that we can compute an approximate posterior distribution
\pi^{y^{(\ell)},\mathrm{alg}}
, from which we can sample

\{\widetilde{u}^{(\ell,n)}\}_{n=1}^{N},

where
\widetilde{u}^{(\ell,n)}\sim\pi^{y^{(\ell)},\mathrm{alg}}
.

Given this Data Assumption and Theorem
1.26
, simulation-based calibration evaluates the algorithm that generates the approximate posteriors
\pi^{y^{(\ell)},\mathrm{alg}}
by verifying whether
\operatorname{rank}(\{f(\widetilde{u}^{(\ell,n)})\}_{n=1}^{N},f(u^{(\ell)}))
, for
\ell=1,\ldots,L
, are approximately uniformly distributed on
[[0,N]]
. If the empirical distribution of the ranks deviates significantly from uniformity, this indicates that the approximate posteriors are not well calibrated.

1.5
Model Error

The underlying concept behind this entire section is that the data
y
, which we use
to determine unknown parameter
u
, is not actually generated by the identity (
1.1
).
This arises, for example, when the forward model
G(\cdot)
does not exactly represent the physical
reality that gave rise to the data; or when the forward model
G(\cdot)
is approximated by
a computer model that does not match the forward model exactly. Indeed, typically both of these
issues arise. In such situation the set-up suffers
from
model error
; it is also another form of
model misspecification
.
We describe three approaches to addressing this issue.
The reader should note that it is also possible to combine
each of the three approaches to devise more general approaches to model error.

The starting point for all three approaches is that the data
y
arises
from noisy observation of a physical process
G_{\mathrm{p}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
so that

(1.19)

y=G_{\mathrm{p}}(u)+\eta.

The assumption is that
G_{\mathrm{p}}
is not available to us, but that we have
a (family of) computational model(s)
G_{\mathrm{c}}
which we can use in place of

G_{\mathrm{p}}
to determine
u
from
y.

1.5.1
Representing Error in Data Space

This first approach makes the assumption that
G_{\mathrm{p}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
is related to computer code
G_{\mathrm{c}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
in the sense that

(1.20)

G_{\mathrm{p}}(u)=G_{\mathrm{c}}(u)+b

for some unknown vector
b\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
Furthermore, random variable
\eta
is assumed to be drawn from a centered distribution
\nu_{\sigma}(\cdot)
, known up to a parameter
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
.
Thus model error is present both because of the
unknown shift between
G_{\mathrm{p}}
and
G_{\mathrm{c}}
and because of the unknown parameter
in the distribution of the additive noise.

Rather than just putting a prior
\rho
on
u
, as in Section
1.1
,
we put a prior
\rho
on
(u,b,\sigma).
The inverse problem is now reformulated
as determining the distribution
\pi^{y}
of
(u,b,\sigma)|y.

Combining (
1.19
) and (
1.20
) we obtain

(1.21)

y=G_{\mathrm{c}}(u)+b+\eta,

where
\eta\sim\nu_{\sigma}.

By applying Bayes Theorem
1.3
we obtain

(1.22)

\pi^{y}(u,b,\sigma)=\frac{1}{Z}\nu_{\sigma}\bigl(y-G_{\mathrm{c}}(u)-b\bigr)\rho(u,b,\sigma),

where

Z=Z^{y}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}}\nu_{\sigma}\bigl(y-G_{\mathrm{c}}(u)-b\bigr)\rho(u,b,\sigma)\,du\,db\,d\sigma>0.

Remark 1.28
.

In this setting the prior
\rho(u,b,\sigma)
is typically factored as an independent
product of priors on
u
and on each of the hyperparameters
b
and
\sigma.

∎

Example 1.29
.

The parameter
\sigma
might, for example, be the standard deviation of an isotropic Gaussian density in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
and then
\nu_{\sigma}(y)=\mathcal{N}(y;0,\sigma^{2}I).

∎

1.5.2
Representing Error in Parameter Space

A different approach is to account for model error in parameter space.
To achieve this, parameter
u
is assumed to be perturbed by a draw

\vartheta
from a random variable with distribution
r(\cdot|\beta)
defined through an
unknown parameter
\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}.
Thus we write

(1.23)

y=G(u+\vartheta)+\eta,

assuming in this setting that
\eta\sim\nu
and that
\nu
is completely known.
We also assume that, conditional on
\beta
, parameters
(u,\vartheta,\eta)
are independent a priori and that
prior on
u
is
\rho.
We then put prior
\varrho
on
\beta
and
apply Bayes Theorem
1.3
to obtain

(1.24)

\pi^{y}(u,\vartheta,\beta)=\frac{1}{Z}\mathsf{l}(y|u,\vartheta)\rho(u)r(\vartheta|\beta)\varrho(\beta),

where

Z=Z^{y}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}}\mathsf{l}(y|u,\vartheta)\rho(u)r(\vartheta|\beta)\varrho(\beta)du\,d\vartheta\,d\beta.

A substantial challenge facing this approach
is to be able to integrate out
\vartheta
and learn only the pair
(u,\beta).
Together
(\vartheta,\eta)
define
a noise model, but it is not additive. Integrating out
\vartheta
amounts to seeking
a tractable likelihood
\mathbb{P}(y|u,\beta).

Alternatively, rather than restricting to settings where the likelihood is tractable,
it is possible to use likelihood-free methods such as approximate Bayesian computation.
We will discuss likelihood-free inference in Section
5.2
. Here, we
illustrate the representation of error in parameter space in the setting of a linear forward model, in a case
where
\mathbb{P}(y|u,\beta)
is tractable.

Example 1.30
.

For a linear forward model,
G(u)=Au
, the data model
(
1.23
) becomes

y=Au+(A\vartheta+\eta).

This can be interpreted as a standard linear forward model with the additive error
A\vartheta+\eta
. If
\vartheta
and
\eta
are independent zero-mean Gaussian random variables, the embedded model error corresponds to a likelihood model with inflated variance where the additional variance is related to the forward model. Then
\mathsf{l}(y|u,\beta)
is determined
by

\mathbb{P}(y|u,\beta)=\mathcal{N}\bigl(Au,AC(\beta)A^{\top}+\Sigma\bigr),

where

C(\beta)=\text{Cov}(\vartheta),\quad\Sigma=\text{Cov}(\eta).

∎

1.5.3
Parameterizing the Forward Model

A third approach to model error postulates that the physically realizable
forward map
G_{\mathrm{p}}
is related to a class of computational models

G_{\mathrm{c}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
in the sense that, for all
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

and for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{p},

(1.25)

G_{\mathrm{p}}(u)=G_{\mathrm{c}}(u,\alpha).

It is assumed that
\alpha
is not known to us. Thus the problem becomes
one of determining the pair
(u,\alpha)
from the observation
y.

Combining (
1.19
) and (
1.25
) suggests consideration of
the Bayesian inverse problem of determining the distribution
of
(u,\alpha)|y
given that

(1.26)

y=G_{\mathrm{c}}(u,\alpha)+\eta.

Here
\eta
again describes additive noise with known distribution
\nu.

Applying Bayes Theorem
1.3
we obtain

(1.27)

\pi^{y}(u,\alpha)=\frac{1}{Z}\nu\bigl(y-G_{\mathrm{c}}(u,\alpha)\bigr)\rho(u,\alpha),

where

Z=Z^{y}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}}\nu\bigl(y-G_{\mathrm{c}}(u,\alpha)\bigr)\rho(u,\alpha)\,du\,d\alpha>0.

Similarly to Remark
1.28
the prior is usually factored as an independent product
of priors on
u
and on
\alpha.

1.6
Surrogate Modeling

One of the earliest, and still one of the most prevalent,
uses of machine learning in the field of computational inverse problems is
surrogate modeling.
In this section we focus on this topic in the context of solving the inverse problem for
u
given
y
,
defined by (
1.1
). This serves as an introduction to the use of machine
learning in inverse problems, a topic we expand upon in the next four chapters.

The key idea is to approximate
G:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
with a cheap-to-evaluate surrogate,
G_{\delta}
, using supervised learning from Chapter
12
. Thus, to learn this approximation, we make the following assumption.

Data Assumption 1.31
.

Data is available in the form

(1.28)

\displaystyle\Bigl\{(u^{(n)},y^{(n)})\Bigr\}_{n=1}^{N},

where the
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}

are generated i.i.d. from probability density function
\Upsilon\in\mathcal{P}(D)
, for some
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d},
and where

y^{(n)}=G(u^{(n)}).

Remark 1.32
.

A key point to appreciate is that, since we will use approximation of
G
to solve the Bayesian
inverse problem for
\pi^{y}
given by Bayes Theorem
1.3
, an ideal choice for
\Upsilon
is that
it is close to
\pi^{y}.
This, of course, leads to a chicken–egg issue. In practice this can be addressed
by choosing
\Upsilon
to have generous support, aiming to subsume that of
\pi^{y};
or by
generating supervised learning data in tandem with solving the inverse problem, in an iterative fashion.
∎

In Subsection
1.6.1
we discuss the use of surrogate forward models
to speed up Bayesian inversion. Subsection
1.6.2
studies the effect of approximating the forward model on the posterior. In Subsection
1.6.3
we comment on surrogate modeling in the context of
Bayesian inversion in the presence of model error;
this requires a generalization of Data Assumption
1.31
.

1.6.1
Accelerating Bayesian Inversion

Recall the inverse problem (
1.1
) of finding

u
from
y
where

y=G(u)+\eta,

under the setting of Assumption
1.1
and Data Assumption
1.2
.
If the Bayesian approach is adopted then methods such as
MCMC, introduced in Subsection
1.2.2
and discussed in detail in Section
15.6
, will need to be used to sample the posterior. Generating each new sample typically requires evaluating the likelihood, and hence the forward model
G.
For instance, in the Metropolis–Hastings Algorithm
6
the likelihood needs to be evaluated to compute the acceptance probability (
15.26
). When
G
is computationally
expensive to evaluate, the multiple evaluations of
G
may be prohibitively
expensive. We address
this issue by using a cheap computational surrogate
G_{\delta}
. While optimization algorithms for MAP estimation often require much fewer forward model evaluations for convergence than MCMC sampling algorithms, surrogate models can also be helpful in classical optimization-based approaches to inverse problems. For instance, surrogate models can facilitate the use of gradient-based optimization algorithms (see Chapter
15
) in applications where gradients of
G
are not available but gradients of the surrogate model
G_{\delta}
can easily be computed.

The methods described in Chapter
12

can be used to approximate the
(scalar-valued) likelihood
\mathsf{l}
resulting from
G
,
by an approximation
\mathsf{l}_{\delta}

with small error
\delta,
uniformly over bounded open
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
; see the discussion
in Section
12.5
.
In Chapter
12
we focus on learning functions taking values
in
\mathbb{R};
however all such methods can
be generalized to approximate vector-valued
G.

This leads to uniform approximation over
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
of
G
by
G_{\delta}
, which
in turn results in a uniform approximation
\mathsf{l}_{\delta}
of the likelihood
\mathsf{l}
.

Such an approximation
G_{\delta}

can be learned under the setting of Data Assumption
1.31
.
If carefully designed,
the machine learning approximation of the likelihood will be much
faster to evaluate than the true likelihood and, because of the
approximation properties, will result in accurate posterior inference,
with errors of size
\delta.

Such results rely on error bounds discussed in Section
12.5
. These approximations
can often be obtained with a number
N
of evaluations
of the forward model which is orders of magnitude smaller than the number of evaluations
required within MCMC, or
even MAP estimation in the context of learning model error (see Subsection
1.6.3
below).
The resulting method can thus be very efficient. The next subsection describes
underpinning theory to transfer approximation of the forward model to approximation
of the posterior.

1.6.2
Posterior Approximation Theorem

We now establish that the approximate posterior resulting from approximating
the likelihood is
indeed close to the true posterior. We employ a modification of the
well-posedness theory from Section
1.3
.

For notational convenience we drop the dependence of the likelihood on
y
in the remainder of this chapter.
Specifically we let

\mathsf{l}(u)=\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{l}_{\delta}(u)=\nu\bigl(y-G_{\delta}(u)\bigr)

denote the true and approximate likelihoods. Thus we may define
the true and approximate posterior distributions by

\pi^{y}(u)=\frac{1}{Z}\mathsf{l}(u)\rho(u)\quad\text{and}\quad\pi_{\delta}^{y}(u)=\frac{1}{Z_{\delta}}\mathsf{l}_{\delta}(u)\rho(u);

here
Z,Z_{\delta}
are the corresponding normalizing constants.
To prove the closeness in Hellinger distance between the true posterior and the posterior with machine-learned likelihood, we will rely on the following assumption.

Assumption 1.33
.

The prior distribution on
u
with density
\rho
is supported on bounded open set
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}

and the data defined by Assumption
1.1
and Data Assumption
1.2

has positive probability under the resulting joint distribution on
(u,y)
, so that
Z>0.
Furthermore, there exist
\delta^{+}>0
and
K_{1},K_{2}\in(0,\infty)
such that, for all
\delta\in(0,\delta^{+}),

(i)

\sup_{u\in D}|\sqrt{\mathsf{l}(u)}-\sqrt{\mathsf{l}_{\delta}(u)}|\leq K_{1}\delta;

(ii)

\sup_{u\in D}(|\sqrt{\mathsf{l}(u)}|+|\sqrt{\mathsf{l}_{\delta}(u)}|)\leq K_{2}.

Remark 1.34
.

Parts (i) and (ii) of Assumption
1.33
can be verified for surrogate models obtained using training data of the form in Data Assumption
1.31
. Approximation-theoretic results for supervised learning are discussed in
Section
12.5
.
∎

Note also that combining (i) and (ii) gives

(1.29)

\sup_{u\in D}|\mathsf{l}(u)-\mathsf{l}_{\delta}(u)|\leq K_{1}K_{2}\delta.

Using Assumption
1.33

we may state the following approximation result
for the machine-learned posterior.
When combined with Lemma
11.3
, the following theorem
guarantees that expectations computed with respect to the machine-learned
posterior incur an error of order
\delta
, the magnitude of the error
in the approximate likelihood.

Theorem 1.35
.

Under Assumption
1.33

there exist
\Delta,c>0
such that, for all
\delta\in(0,\Delta)
,

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\pi^{y},\pi_{\delta}^{y})\leq c\delta.

Proof.

As in the proof of the well-posedness Theorem
1.23
, it can be shown that there exist

\Delta,c_{1},c_{2}>0

such that, for all
\delta\in(0,\Delta)
,

(1.30)

|Z-Z_{\delta}|\leq c_{1}\delta\quad\text{and}\quad Z,Z_{\delta}\geq c_{2}.

Next, also following proof of the well-posedness Theorem
1.23
, we
decompose the total error into two contributions, reflecting respectively
the difference between
Z
and
Z_{\delta}
, and the difference between
the likelihoods
\mathsf{l}
and
\mathsf{l}_{\delta}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\pi^{y},\pi_{\delta}^{y})\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Biggl\|\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho}{Z}}-\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho}{Z_{\delta}}}\Biggr\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Biggl\|\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho}{Z_{\delta}}}-\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}_{\delta}\rho}{Z_{\delta}}}\Biggr\|_{L^{2}}.

Here
L^{2}=L^{2}(D;\mathbb{R}).
It then follows from (
1.30
) that, for
\delta\in(0,\Delta)
,

\displaystyle\Biggl\|\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho}{Z}}-\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho}{Z_{\delta}}}\Biggr\|_{L^{2}}=

\displaystyle\ \Biggl|\frac{1}{\sqrt{Z}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{Z_{\delta}}}\Biggr|\Bigg(\int_{D}\mathsf{l}(u)\rho(u)\,du\Bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{|Z-Z_{\delta}|}{(\sqrt{Z}+\sqrt{Z_{\delta}})\sqrt{Z_{\delta}}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{c_{1}}{2c_{2}}\delta.

And from Assumption
1.33
we have that

\Biggl\|\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho}{Z_{\delta}}}-\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}_{\delta}\rho}{Z_{\delta}}}\Biggr\|_{L^{2}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{Z_{\delta}}}\Bigg(\int_{D}\Bigl|\sqrt{\mathsf{l}(u)}-\sqrt{\mathsf{l}_{\delta}(u)}\Bigr|^{2}\rho(u)\,du\Bigg)^{1/2}\leq\sqrt{\frac{K_{1}^{2}}{c_{2}}}\delta.

Therefore

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\pi^{y},\pi_{\delta}^{y})\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{c_{1}}{2c_{2}}\delta+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{\frac{K_{1}^{2}}{c_{2}}}\delta=c\delta,

with
c=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{c_{1}}{2c_{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{\frac{K_{1}^{2}}{c_{2}}}
independent of
\delta
.
∎

1.6.3
Accelerating Bayesian Inversion in the Presence of Model Error

We argued in Subsection
1.6.1
for the potential use of
surrogate modeling within
MCMC, where multiple forward evaluations are typically required.
Even when MAP
estimation is used, multiple evaluations of
G
may be needed to optimize
the objective function. Furthermore, if model error is being jointly
learned, in the setting of Subsection
1.5.3
,
then multiple MAP estimators may be
required, leading to many more evaluations.
This subsection is centered on surrogate modeling for MAP estimation when model error is also being learned.

Recall the Bayesian inverse problem to jointly estimate unknown parameter
u
and unknown model error parameter
\alpha
defined in (
1.27
).
Thus we have access to a family of computational models
G_{\mathrm{c}}(\cdot\,;\alpha):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
parameterized by
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{p}.
Our data assumption is as follows:

Data Assumption 1.36
.

Data is available in the form

(1.31)

\displaystyle\Bigl\{(u^{(n)},\alpha^{(n)},y^{(n)})\Bigr\}_{n=1}^{N},

where the
\{(u^{(n)},\alpha^{(n)})\}_{n=1}^{N}

are generated i.i.d. from probability density function
\Upsilon\otimes q\in\mathcal{P}(D\times\mathbb{R}^{p})
, for some
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d},
and where

y^{(n)}=G_{\mathrm{c}}(u^{(n)},\alpha^{(n)}).

From this data we may learn a cheap surrogate for
G_{\mathrm{c}},
using
the techniques of Chapter
12
. The MAP estimation problem,
in the context of Subsection
1.5.3
, requires minimization, over the pair

(u,\alpha)
, of

{\mathsf{J}}(u,\alpha)=-\log\nu\bigl(y-G_{\mathrm{c}}(u,\alpha)\bigr)-\log\rho(u,\alpha).

Some methods for this problem work by alternating minimization over

u
and over
\alpha
; a prototypical such method is to iterate, for
\ell

until convergence, starting from initial
\alpha^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:

(1.32a)

\displaystyle u^{\ell+1}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathsf{J}}\bigl(u,\alpha^{\ell}\bigr),

(1.32b)

\displaystyle\alpha^{\ell+1}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}{\mathsf{J}}\bigl(u^{\ell+1},\alpha\bigr).

Generalizations of this alternating strategy are possible; for example a finite number
of steps (possibly
\ell
-dependent) of gradient descent may be substituted for the two minimizations.
For each
\ell,
multiple evaluations of
G_{\mathrm{c}}(\cdot,\cdot)
may be required
and use of a surrogate may make the method more efficient.

Finally, we point out that similar ideas to accelerate Bayesian inversion in the presence of model error apply when computing posterior summaries via sampling rather than a MAP estimator via optimization. For sampling the joint posterior distribution over the pair
(u,\alpha)
, a natural approach is given by the Gibbs sampler from Subsection
15.6.2
. As in (
1.32
), the Gibbs sampler would then alternate between sampling the conditional of
u
given
\alpha
and the conditional of
\alpha
given
u.
Such a Gibbs sampler would also directly benefit from use of cheap surrogate
G_{\mathrm{c}}.

Bibliography

[

given in

1.9
overlap: the regularity of
u^{\mathsf{true}}
is such that it is not a draw from
the prior, with probability one, and the effect of this on rates of contraction is studied.
For classical optimization-based
approaches to inverse problems,
we refer to the books and lecture notes
[

,

,

,

,

]
.

[

,

,

]
.
For generalizations see the papers
[

,

,

]
.
Monte Carlo methods are a broad class of approaches for characterizing posterior distributions using a sum of random Dirac measures; see
[

,

,

]
for textbooks on the theoretical and computational aspects of these methods. Quasi Monte Carlo (QMC) methods blend ideas from deterministic quadrature
with the high dimensional benefits of Monte Carlo; see
[
90
,
369
]
.
While direct Monte Carlo sampling is possible in some settings, Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) is a variant that is used in practice when it is not possible to sample exactly from the posterior.
A general introduction to MCMC may
be found in
[
187
]
. Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC)
[
143
,
126
]
is a methodology
which proceeds by introducing an interpolation from prior to posterior and incrementally steps
through a sequence of inverse problems to connect them
[
62
,
63
,
272
]
. We provide a brief overview of MCMC in Section
15.6
and discuss the use of learned transport maps to accelerate MCMC in Section
4.5
. Additional bibliographical remarks on MCMC can be found in Sections
4.6
and
15.8
.

The fact that the conditional mean minimizes the mean-square error is a classic result in probability; see, for example,
[
509
]
. A characterization of the class of
deterministic scoring rules which the conditional mean minimizes is given in
[
45
]
.

The stability and well-posedness
of Bayesian inverse problems was
first studied in
[
333
]
, using
the Kullback–Leibler divergence (see Definition
11.39
) and focusing on perturbations in the data.
The articles
[

,

]

study similar stability and well-posedness results
in the Hellinger metric, as we do here; application to perturbations arising from numerical
approximation of the forward model
G
may be found in
[

]
.
Related results on stability and well-posedness,
but using other distances and divergences,
may be found in
[
302
]
.
The papers
[

,

]
discuss generalizations of the well-posedness theory to various classes of specific
non-Gaussian priors. Simulation-based calibration was introduced in
[
467
]
, and the proof of Theorem
1.26
is presented therein.

The approach to model error outlined in Subsection
1.5.1
was introduced
in
[
276
]
.
The embedded model error framework of Subsection
1.5.2
was introduced in
[
430
]
; for an introduction to approximate
Bayesian computation see
[
440
]
. An example of
the approach to model error described in Subsection
1.5.3

may be found in
[
131
]
.
A parameterized forward model may be a coarse-scale model where unresolved small-scale physical processes are described by parameters
\alpha
. (For example,
\alpha
may describe cloud cover in a climate model
G_{\mathrm{c}}
.) More examples will be provided in Chapter
8
.
Methodologies to identify parameters of structural error models (e.g., within computational models for dynamical processes), often using indirect data, are described in
[
512
,
312
]
.

Using emulators to speed up forward model evaluations, for
example in the context of likelihood evaluation in Bayesian inversion, was first introduced as a systematic methodology in
[
418
]
,
and taken further in the realm of Bayesian model error estimation
in
[
276
]
. The paper
[
456
]

studies the use of Gaussian processes for emulation, and derives
error bounds quantifying the effect of emulation error
on the posterior. The methodology is developed for a range of applications
in the geosciences in
[
128
]
; in particular that paper addresses the issue
of how to determine
\Upsilon
in tandem with solution of the inverse problem as discussed
in Remark
1.32
.
A specific application of the idea in climate science may
be found in
[
153
]
. Data-driven discretizations of forward models for Bayesian inversion are studied in
[
67
]
.
A recent approach to directly learning parameter to solution (forward) and solution to parameter (inverse)
surrogate maps may be found in
[
482
]
. Surrogate models are also useful in optimization approaches to inverse problems. In this regard, Bayesian optimization techniques
[
179
]

replace an expensive-to-evaluate objective function with a surrogate model, and gradually improve this surrogate model along the optimization process by judiciously acquiring new training data. We refer to
[
280
,
279
]
for a discussion of Bayesian optimization for MAP estimation in Bayesian inverse problems.

An important aspect of learning forward models is to choose the supervised training data over which we would like to find an accurate surrogate model. While we would ideally like to be accurate over the support of the posterior, finding such a surrogate model requires being able to sample the posterior. Instead, it is common to seek a surrogate model that is accurate over the support of the prior. To address this, several recent methods use approximate posteriors to iteratively refine the approximation of the surrogate model
[
128
,
234
]
.

Chapter 2
Variational Inference

A problem has a variational formulation
if its solution can be written as the minimizer of an objective function.
The variational formulation of any problem in mathematics is useful because it
opens the door to computational methods. Such computational methods seek to minimize the objective
function over a strict subset of the space on which the original variational problem is posed.
If the strict subset is chosen judiciously (this is a problem-dependent
choice) then the resulting computational methodology is both tractable and yields
a useful and interpretable
approximation of the solution of the original variational problem
posed on the whole space.

This chapter is devoted to formulating Bayes Theorem
1.3

variationally and using this formulation as the basis for approximate inference.
We continue to work under Data Assumption
1.2
.
Under this data assumption, and given a prior distribution on
u
, the posterior distribution on
u|y
is defined by Bayes Theorem
1.3
.
In Section
2.1
we show how this theorem can be formulated
variationally as a minimization problem over the set of all probability density
functions. This leads to the core methodology of variational inference. In
Section
2.2
we present two canonical choices of subsets of all probability density functions over which to approximate the posterior: the mean-field and Gaussian subsets. In Section
2.3
we discuss
some key properties of variational inference, studying other choices of optimization
problem which yield the posterior as a minimizer, and linking to the evidence lower bound. We conclude the chapter, in Section
2.4
, with bibliographical remarks.

2.1
Variational Formulation of Bayes Theorem

Our starting point in this section is Bayes Theorem
1.3
. Because
dependence on
y
is not central to our discussions here, we write the posterior
as
\pi:=\pi^{y}.

A useful formulation of Bayes Theorem arises from viewing the posterior as the solution of an optimization problem over the space of probability density functions. In what follows we recall from the Preface that
\mathcal{P}:=\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denotes the space
of all probability density functions over
\mathbb{R}^{d}.
We define an objective

{\mathsf{F}}:\mathcal{P}\to\mathbb{R}
which, when evaluated at a candidate density
q
, quantifies the discrepancy between the posterior density
\pi
and
q
.
The objective is constructed to be minimized at
q=\pi
. By using
Bayes Theorem
1.3
the objective function can be rewritten in terms
of the prior, the likelihood
and the normalization
constant.

One natural class of objective functions
{\mathsf{F}}
is defined by the family
of
\mathsf{f}
-divergences
\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{f}}(q\|\pi):

{\mathsf{F}}(q):=\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{f}}(q\|\pi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{f}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{q(u)}{\pi(u)}}}\right)\pi(u)\,du.

See Subsection
11.2.1
for the definition and properties of this family of
distance-like objects defined on the space of probability densities.
By the properties of all divergences, detailed at the start of Section
11.2
, we deduce that
{\mathsf{F}}(q)

is minimized at
q=\pi
and that this is the unique minimizer.
A particularly useful choice arises from setting
\mathsf{f}(t)=t\log(t),

leading to definition of the objective

(2.1)

{\mathsf{F}}(q):=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|\pi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{q(u)}{\pi(u)}}}\right)q(u)\,du,

the Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence. We discuss
the reason for making this particular choice in Remark
2.2
below.
Having chosen to work with the KL divergence (
2.1
),
the following theorem establishes a variational formulation of
Bayes Theorem
1.3
.

Theorem 2.1
.

Consider the Bayesian inverse problem defined
by prior
\rho
and likelihood
\mathsf{l}
given by (
1.2
),
under Data Assumption
1.2
. Define
{\mathsf{J}}:\mathcal{P}\to\mathbb{R}
by

(2.2a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(q)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|\rho)-\mathbb{E}^{q}[\log\mathsf{l}(y|\cdot)],

(2.2b)

\displaystyle q_{\text{\sc{opt}}}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{q\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathsf{J}}(q).

Then
q_{\text{\sc{opt}}}=\pi,
the posterior distribution.

Proof.

By the properties of all divergences, detailed at the start of Section
11.2
,
it follows that
{\mathsf{F}}(\cdot)=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\cdot\,\|\pi)
is non-negative for all inputs from the set of probability density functions and has a unique minimizer over
\mathcal{P}
at
q=\pi.

Using the form of the density, we see that

(2.3a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{F}}(q)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{q}[\log q-\log\rho-\log\mathsf{l}(y|\cdot)+\log Z]

(2.3b)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|\rho)-\mathbb{E}^{q}[\log\mathsf{l}(y|\cdot)]+\log Z

(2.3c)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{J}}(q)+\log Z.

Because
Z
is a constant with respect to
q
, the minimizer of
{\mathsf{F}}(\cdot)
, which
is unique, is equivalent to the minimizer of
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)
and the proof is complete.
∎

Remark 2.2
.

The specific choice (
2.1
) of
\mathsf{f}
-divergence is convenient as then the objective
\mathsf{F}(q)
agrees, up to an additive constant independent of
q
, with the objective
\mathsf{J}(q)
in (
2.2
a), whose definition does not involve the normalization constant of the posterior. This fact is established in equation (
2.3
) while Remark
11.41
discusses why KL is the
only

\mathsf{f}
-divergence for which this property holds.
In Subsection
2.3.1
, we discuss reversing the order of
q
and
\pi
in the arguments of the KL divergence,
corresponding to making the choice
\mathsf{f}(t)=-\log(t).

Note that, using the loss function (
1.9
), we may write

(2.4)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(q)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{q}[\mathsf{L}]+\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|\rho).

Using this formulation of
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)
we comment on the structure of the minimization
problem (
2.2
) in relation to the MAP estimation problem presented in Section
1.2
. The MAP problem requires minimization of (
1.11
).
Comparison with (
2.4
) shows that in both cases the objective involves two terms that balance the fit to the data (the first term) with properties of the prior distribution (the second term). Furthermore the first term in (
2.4
) is the
expectation of the first term in (
1.11
). Note that minimization of (
2.4
) is more general than minimization of (
1.11
): the former provides a probability density function rather than the point estimator provided by the latter.

Both the MAP objective function (
1.11
) and the probabilistic objective function (
2.4
) are defined by
the prior and likelihood, and avoid calculation of the normalization constant – see Remark
1.11
.
As discussed in Remark
1.21
avoiding calculation of the normalization constant
is also a key feature of the Metropolis–Hastings sampling algorithm.
∎

2.2
Canonical Approaches to Variational Inference

In practice, characterizing the posterior distribution is difficult in general.
One approach to doing so is to approximate the posterior using the variational formulation. We replace the set
\mathcal{P}
in (
2.2
) by a tractable class of probability measures
\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathcal{P}
for the purpose of computations and for the
purpose of revealing an explicit, interpretable form. The variational inference
problem is then given by solving the following optimization problem:

(2.5)

q^{\star}\in\argmin_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}{\mathsf{J}}(q).

Noting the form (
2.2
a) of
{\mathsf{J}}(q)
this optimization problem can be implemented, approximately,
provided we are able to evaluate the prior

\rho(u)
and the likelihood
\mathsf{l}(y|u)
for any
pair
(u,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}.

However, typically some quadrature will be needed, in particular
to evaluate expectations against
q(\cdot\,\theta)
from some parameterized class
\mathcal{Q}.

In the next two subsections we outline two canonical classes of tractable distributions
\mathcal{Q}
: in Subsection
2.2.1
the products of one-dimensional marginal densities;
and in Subsection
2.2.2
multivariate Gaussians. The former case is known as
mean-field variational inference
and the latter as
Gaussian variational inference.

2.2.1
Mean-Field Family

Mean-field inference works with a family
\mathcal{Q}
