of distributions that factorize into a product of independent components. While this choice does not represent dependencies among its variables, it is often chosen because of the efficiency of methods for finding
q^{\star}
in this case, and because of the ease
of computing marginal properties once
q^{\star}
is determined.

Definition 2.3
.

The probability density function
q
of a random variable
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is in the
mean-field
family
\mathcal{Q}
if the coordinates of
u
are independent. That is,
q
can be written in the form

q(u)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}q_{i}(u_{i}),

where
q_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})
denotes the probability
density function of the
i
th coordinate
u_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
of
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
∎

With this choice of
\mathcal{Q}
the solution of the optimization problem in (
2.5
) may be approached by using the
following consistency equations, relating the marginal densities

\{q_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
to one another.
We employ the notation
u_{-i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
described in the Preface.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be defined as in Definition
2.3
.
Then the optimal

q^{\star}(u)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}q^{\star}_{i}(u_{i})

solving optimization problem (
2.5
) satisfies

q^{\star}_{i}(u_{i})\propto\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\log\pi(u)\prod_{j\neq i}q^{\star}_{j}(u_{j})\,du_{-i}}}\right)\quad\text{ for }i=1,\dots,d.

Proof.

Recall that minimizing
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)
is equivalent to minimizing

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\cdot\|\pi)
. The KL divergence
\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|\pi)
for a density
q
in the mean-field family is given by

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|\pi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i=1}^{d}q_{i}(u_{i})}}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{d}q_{i}(u_{i})\,du-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\pi(u)\prod_{i=1}^{d}q_{i}(u_{i})\,du

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\log q_{i}(u_{i})q_{i}(u_{i})\,du_{i}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\pi(u)\prod_{i=1}^{d}q_{i}(u_{i})\,du,

where in the last line we used that each integrand only depends on
u_{i}
and each
q_{i}
is a probability density function that integrates to
1
. Taking the first variation of the KL divergence with respect to each
q_{i},
we have

\frac{\delta}{\delta q_{i}}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|\pi)=1+\log q_{i}(u_{i})-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\log\pi(u)\prod_{j\neq i}q_{j}(u_{j})\,du_{-i}.

Here, notice that including a Lagrange multiplier to enforce the normalization that
q_{i}
integrates to
1
changes the right-hand side by an additive constant independent of
u_{i}
, which is absorbed into the proportionality constant.
Setting the first variation equal to zero and rearranging the terms gives us the un-normalized form for each marginal density
q_{i}^{\star}
at any critical point of
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)

over
\mathcal{Q}.

∎

Remark 2.5
.

We note that although the preceding proposition is formulated in terms of
the posterior density, its statement remains unchanged if
\pi(u)
is replaced
by
\rho(u)\mathsf{l}(y|u)
; this simply changes the constant of proportionality. Thus, to use it,
we do not need to know the normalization constant
Z
in Bayes Theorem
1.3
.
∎

The proposition defines a set of coupled equations that must be satisfied by the optimal marginal densities in terms of the other marginal densities. Moreover, it prescribes how to set
q_{i}
when keeping all other coordinates fixed. Thus, a natural approach to find the minimizer is to perform coordinate updates on each marginal. This algorithm is known as
coordinate-ascent variational inference
(CAVI).
Ascent
because the method is traditionally formulated
in terms of maximizing the
\mathsf{ELBO}
functional (see Definition
15.25
) rather than minimizing the KL divergence; we prefer, for consistency with other optimization problems
in the notes, to formulate it in terms of minimizing
{\mathsf{J}}
, and hence
descent.

The updates are thus, sequentially for iteration index
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
until convergence, and for

i=1,\dots,d\,,

(2.6)

q^{\ell+1}_{i}(u_{i})\propto\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\log\pi(u)\prod_{j<i}q^{\ell+1}_{j}(u_{j})\prod_{j>i}q^{\ell}_{j}(u_{j})\,du_{-i}}}\right).

Algorithm 1

Coordinate-Ascent Variational Inference

1:

Input
: Density
\pi
known up to normalizing constant. Initialization
q^{0}.
Number
L
of iterations.

2:

For
\ell=0,1,2,\ldots,L-1,
compute
q^{\ell+1}
from
q^{\ell}:

3:

Update
q_{i}^{\ell+1}
for
i=1,\ldots,d
using (
2.6
).

4:

Output
: Approximation
q^{L}
of density
\pi.

Remark 2.6
.

The mean-field methodology has the computational advantage of reducing the inference problem
in (potentially high) dimension
d
to one of
d
independent one-dimensional inference problems.
As such it can potentially accurately learn marginal information on specific coordinates; but it cannot
learn correlations. In the next subsection we work in a different subset
\mathcal{Q}
, the set of Gaussian
measures, in which it is possible to approximate correlation information.
∎

Remark 2.7
.

We make several remarks about the practical computation of the expectation in (
2.6
). In the case that the conditional distributions
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(u_{i}|u_{-i},y)
are in the exponential family, the update (
2.6
) can be computed in closed form, facilitating the scalability of the method. See the Bibliography Section
2.4
for more details.
In other cases, if the one-dimensional marginals
q_{i}
are easy to sample from, the expectation can be computed via Monte Carlo. That is, at iteration
\ell
and for component
i
, obtain samples
\{u_{j}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
, where
u_{j}^{(n)}\sim q_{j}^{\ell+1}
for
j<i
and
u_{j}^{(n)}\sim q_{j}^{\ell}
for
j>i
. Then, the update (
2.6
) can be approximated as

q^{\ell+1}_{i}(u_{i})\propto\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\log\pi(u)\prod_{j<i}q^{\ell+1}_{j}(u_{j})\prod_{j>i}q^{\ell}_{j}(u_{j})\,du_{-i}}}\right)\approx\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\log\pi(u^{(n)})}}\right),

where
u^{(n)}=(u_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,u_{i},\ldots,u_{d}^{(n)})
. However, note that this method may require many evaluations of the unnormalized density, which will be costly when the likelihood evaluations are expensive.
∎

2.2.2
Gaussian Distributions

Another tractable variational approach is to seek an approximate distribution
q^{\star}

from within a parametric family
\mathcal{Q}
of probability density functions. In many applications, interest
is focused on ultimately estimating the first two moments of
\pi
; in
this context a natural family to consider is Gaussian distributions.
In this subsection we derive optimality conditions for the solution to the resulting optimization problem.
To find a Gaussian approximation, we seek to minimize
\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\cdot\|\pi)
over the set of distributions

(2.7a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}:=

\displaystyle\bigl\{q\in\mathcal{P}:q=\mathcal{N}(m,\Sigma),(m,\Sigma)\in\mathfrak{C}\bigr\},

(2.7b)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}:=

\displaystyle\bigl\{(m,\Sigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\textrm{sym},>}\bigr\}.

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be defined as in (
2.7
).
Then the optimal

q^{\star}=\mathcal{N}(m^{\star},\Sigma^{\star})
solving (
2.5
) satisfies

(2.8)

(m^{\star},\Sigma^{\star})\in\argmin_{(m,\Sigma)\in\mathfrak{C}}\Bigl(\mathbb{E}^{q}[-\log\pi(\cdot)]-\frac{1}{2}\log\det(\Sigma)\Bigr),

where the expectation is with respect to
q=\mathcal{N}(m,\Sigma).

Proof.

Recall that
q^{\star}
minimizes
\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\cdot\|\pi)
over
\mathcal{Q}.

Recall further that the density
q(u)
of
\mathcal{N}(m,\Sigma)
is given by

(2.9)

q(u)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}\det(\Sigma)^{1/2}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2}|u-m|_{\Sigma}^{2}\Bigr).

We may rewrite any expectation under
q
in terms of a standard Gaussian random variable
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
. Indeed, using the relation
u=m+\Sigma^{1/2}\xi
, it may be shown that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log q(u)\,q(u)\,du=-\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{2}\log(2\pi)-\frac{1}{2}\log\det(\Sigma).

Since

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|\pi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log q(u)\,q(u)\,du-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\pi(u)\,q(u)\,du,

the desired result follows.
∎

Remark 2.9
.

The two terms in the objective function (
2.8
) can be interpreted as having a competitive
behavior and as regularizing the MAP perspective. The first term is minimized by a Gaussian distribution with
zero variance, centered at the MAP estimator of
\pi
,
the point of highest posterior density: a Dirac measure at the posterior mode.
The second term, however, approaches positive infinity if the variance of any marginal approaches zero.
Hence, the second term in the objective regularizes the optimization problem to ensure the
approximating Gaussian is not degenerate in any direction.
∎

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
(m^{\star},\Sigma^{\star})\in\mathfrak{C}
solve the Gaussian variational inference
problem defined by Proposition
2.8
. The solution satisfies the first-order optimality
conditions
1
1

1

Use of
D
and
D^{2}
here denotes derivatives with respect to
u
, then evaluated
at
u=m^{\star}+(\Sigma^{\star})^{1/2}\xi.

\mathbb{E}^{\xi}[D\log\pi(m^{\star}+(\Sigma^{\star})^{1/2}\xi)]=0,\quad\mathbb{E}^{\xi}[D^{2}\log\pi(m^{\star}+(\Sigma^{\star})^{1/2}\xi)]=-(\Sigma^{\star})^{-1},

where
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}).

Proof.

Throughout the proof, use of
D
or
D^{2}
without a subscript is to be taken to denote
derivative(s) with respect to variable
u;
in addition,
q
denotes the Gaussian density

\mathcal{N}(m,\Sigma)
given in (
2.9
). The first-order optimality condition for
the optimization problem in (
2.8
) is given by

D_{(m,\Sigma)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathbb{E}^{q}[-\log\pi(u)]-\frac{1}{2}\log\det(\Sigma)}}\right]}}\right|_{(m^{\star},\Sigma^{\star})}=0.

The gradients of the objective with respect to
m
and
\Sigma
are given by

\displaystyle D_{m}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}-\log\pi(u)q(u)\,du-\frac{1}{2}\log\det(\Sigma)}}\right]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}-\log\pi(u)D_{m}q(u)\,du,

\displaystyle D_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}-\log\pi(u)q(u)\,du-\frac{1}{2}\log\det(\Sigma)}}\right]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}-\log\pi(u)D_{\Sigma}q(u)\,du-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{-1}.

From the symmetry of the Gaussian density with respect to
u
and
m
, we have

\displaystyle D_{m}q(u)

\displaystyle=-Dq(u),

\displaystyle D_{\Sigma}q(u)

\displaystyle=q(u)D_{\Sigma}\log q(u)

\displaystyle=-q(u)\frac{1}{2}[\Sigma^{-1}-\Sigma^{-1}(u-m)(u-m)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}D^{2}q(u).

Substituting these expressions above and applying integration by parts gives us

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}-\log\pi(u)D_{m}q(u)\,du

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\pi(u)Dq(u)\,du=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D\log\pi(u)q(u)\,du,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}-\log\pi(u)D_{\Sigma}q(u)\,du

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\pi(u)D^{2}q(u)\,du=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D^{2}\log\pi(u)q(u)\,du.

Setting the two gradients equal to zero and re-writing the expectations in terms of a standard Gaussian random variable
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
using the relation
u=m+\Sigma^{1/2}\xi
for a positive definite covariance
\Sigma
gives us the result.
∎

Remark 2.11
.

If
-c^{-}I_{d}\preceq D^{2}\log\pi(u)\preceq-c^{+}I_{d}
for all
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then the optimal solution satisfies
\frac{1}{c^{+}}I_{d}\preceq\Sigma^{\star}\preceq\frac{1}{c^{-}}I_{d}
.
∎

Example 2.12
.

We work in the setting of the inverse problem defined in Example
1.14
:
the observational noise is
\nu=\mathcal{N}({0},{\Gamma})
, for positive definite covariance
\Gamma
,
and the prior is a centered Gaussian

\rho=\mathcal{N}({0},{C})
, where
C
is positive definite.
We consider approximate variational inference in the Gaussian class
and seek
q=\mathcal{N}(m,\Sigma).
In this setting the negative log likelihood has the form, up to an irrelevant additive constant,

-\log\mathsf{l}(y|u)=\frac{1}{2}|{y}-{G}({u})|_{{\Gamma}}^{2}.

Example
11.40
shows that

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|m|^{2}_{C}+\text{Tr}(C^{-1}\Sigma)-d+\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\det C}{\det\Sigma}}}\right)}}\right).

Combining these two facts, abusing notation and writing
{\mathsf{J}}(q)={\mathsf{J}}(m,\Sigma)
and ignoring the irrelevant additive constant,
we find from Theorem
2.1
that

{\mathsf{J}}(m,\Sigma)=\mathbb{E}^{\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)}\frac{1}{2}|y-G(m+\sqrt{\Sigma}\xi)|^{2}_{{\Gamma}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|m|^{2}_{C}+\text{Tr}(C^{-1}\Sigma)-d+\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\det C}{\det\Sigma}}}\right)}}\right).

This should be compared with the objective function in (
1.13
) defining the MAP estimator for the same
inverse problem. Indeed it may be viewed as a form of regularization of that problem.
∎

2.3
Properties of Variational Inference

2.3.1
Mode-Seeking Versus Mean-Seeking Variational Inference

In the preceding section we seek an approximation to the posterior distribution

\pi
by minimizing, over
\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathcal{P}
, the
functional

q\mapsto\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|\pi)=\mathbb{E}_{q}[\log(q/\pi)];

this
is sometimes referred to as the reverse KL divergence. Using the reverse KL divergence as an objective is computationally convenient in the setting where we can evaluate the likelihood and the prior, but
we do not necessarily know the normalization constant, nor do we necessarily have the ability
to easily sample from the posterior. In comparison, the forward KL divergence

q\mapsto\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\pi\|q)=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[\log(\pi/q)]

is more convenient for optimization over
\mathcal{Q}
when we can sample from
\pi
, but do not necessarily have access to an analytical expression for the target density.

In addition to computational considerations, the two choices of KL divergence lead to different behavior when minimizing over a subset of probability measures. The reverse KL favors approximate distributions where
\log(q/\pi)
is small in regions of high probability of
q
, that is when
q\approx\pi
where
q
is large, or in regions where
q
is much smaller than
\pi
. As a result, the minimizers for
q
tend to fit one mode of multi-modal distributions
\pi
, but miss the other modes. Hence, minimizing the reverse KL is also known as
mode-seeking
. In contrast, the minimizers of the forward KL favor approximate distributions
q
where
\log(\pi/q)
is small in regions of high probability of
\pi
. This occurs by having
q
be non-zero everywhere in the support of
\pi
so the denominator in the log does not approach zero. Placing mass everywhere in the support of
\pi
leads to a
mean-seeking
behavior, where the minimizer for
q
corresponds to matching the moments of the target density
\pi
, for Gaussian variational families.

The following remark shows that mean-seeking behavior is apparent when the set
\mathcal{Q}
comprises Gaussians. The remark also introduces a useful definition for a form of Gaussian projection from the space of all probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
onto the space of Gaussians on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Remark 2.13
.

We write
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for the set
of all Gaussian probability density functions over
\mathbb{R}^{d},
including degenerate Gaussians
with non-invertible covariance; in particular all Dirac measures are contained
in
\mathcal{G}.
Define
{\mathsf{G}}:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{G}
by

{\mathsf{G}}\pi\in\argmin_{\mu\in\mathcal{G}}\,\,\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\pi\|\mu).

It then follows that, in fact,

{\mathsf{G}}\pi=\mathcal{N}(m_{\pi},\Sigma_{\pi}),

where
m_{\pi}
and
\Sigma_{\pi}
are the mean and covariance of random variable
u\sim\pi.

∎

Further discussion on mode-seeking versus mean-seeking variational inference and references to the literature may be found in the bibliography in Section
2.4
.

2.3.2
Evidence Lower Bound

We now relate
{\mathsf{J}}(q)
, defined by (
2.2
a), to an important concept in variational inference, namely the

evidence lower bound
(
\mathsf{ELBO}
) given in Definition
15.25
:

\mathsf{ELBO}(\widetilde{\pi},q)=\mathbb{E}^{q}[\log\widetilde{\pi}(u)]-\mathbb{E}^{q}[\log q(u)].

The reason for the terminology evidence lower bound
will be made clear through Remarks
2.15
,
2.16
.
The evidence lower bound is sometimes also known as the negative variational free energy.

The definition is often used in the situation where
\widetilde{\pi}(u)
is found from
the joint distribution of random variable
(u,y)
, with
y
frozen. This is the setting
of the following proposition, which sheds light on the significance of the
\mathsf{ELBO}
functional.
Recall prior measure
\rho(u)
, likelihood function
\mathsf{l}(y|u)
given by equation (
1.2
) and posterior measure
\pi:=\pi^{y}
given by Theorem
1.3
.

Proposition 2.14
.

Define the unnormalized density
\widetilde{\pi}(u)=\rho(u)\mathsf{l}(y|u)
and define the normalization constant

Z
by
\pi=Z^{-1}\widetilde{\pi},
assuming that
Z>0.

Then, the maximizer

q_{\text{\sc{opt}}}\in\argmax_{q\in\mathcal{P}}\mathsf{ELBO}(\widetilde{\pi},q)

is attained at the posterior distribution
q_{\text{\sc{opt}}}=\pi
. Furthermore, the maximum value
is
\mathsf{ELBO}(\widetilde{\pi},q_{\text{\sc{opt}}})=\log(Z).

Proof.

This follows from noting that

\displaystyle\mathsf{ELBO}(\widetilde{\pi},q)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{q}[\log\pi(u)]-\mathbb{E}^{q}[\log q(u)]+\mathbb{E}^{q}[\log\widetilde{\pi}(u)]-\mathbb{E}^{q}[\log\pi(u)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{q}[\log\pi(u)]-\mathbb{E}^{q}[\log q(u)]+\mathbb{E}^{q}[\log(Z)]

\displaystyle=-\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|\pi)+\log(Z).

Since
Z
is independent of
q
, optimality requires that
\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q_{\text{\sc{opt}}}\|\pi)=0
, and
hence that
q_{\text{\sc{opt}}}=\pi.
As a consequence
\mathsf{ELBO}(\widetilde{\pi},q_{\text{\sc{opt}}})=\log(Z)
.
∎

Remark 2.15
.

In the preceding proposition
\widetilde{\pi}(u)=\mathbb{P}(u,y)
is the joint distribution and the normalizing constant
Z=\mathbb{P}(y)
is called the
evidence
. Thus the preceding proposition demonstrates the significance of the quantity
\mathsf{ELBO}(\widetilde{\pi},q_{\text{\sc{opt}}})
: it delivers the logarithm of the evidence, namely the log-probability that the observed data
y
was produced by the statistical model for the joint random variable
(u,y)
defined by equation (
1.1
) and
Assumption
1.1
. It is natural to ask what we can learn about the evidence if we perform variational inference over subset
\mathcal{Q}
of
\mathcal{P};
this is the subject of the next remark.
∎

Remark 2.16
.

Variational inference
is performed by solving the following optimization problem over
\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathcal{P}:

(2.10)

q^{\star}\in\argmin_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}{\mathsf{J}}(q).

We continue with the setting of Proposition
2.14
in which

\pi=Z^{-1}\widetilde{\pi}.

Recall the definitions of
{\mathsf{F}}(\cdot)
from (
2.1
) and note that it may be
extended to unnormalized second argument so that we may write

\displaystyle{\mathsf{F}}(q)=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|\pi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{q(u)}{\pi(u)}}}\right)q(u)\,du

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{q(u)}{\widetilde{\pi}(u)}}}\right)q(u)\,du+\log(Z)

\displaystyle=-\mathsf{ELBO}(\widetilde{\pi},q)+\log(Z).

But by (
2.3
) we have, for
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)
defined by (
2.2
),
{\mathsf{F}}(q)={\mathsf{J}}(q)+\log(Z)
. Hence

{\mathsf{J}}(q)=-\mathsf{ELBO}(\widetilde{\pi},q)

and it follows that

\log(Z)=\mathsf{ELBO}(\widetilde{\pi},q_{\text{\sc{opt}}})\geq\mathsf{ELBO}(\widetilde{\pi},q^{\star})=-{\mathsf{J}}(q^{\star}).

Hence, after variational inference over
{\cal Q}
is performed, we can
estimate the normalizing constant, or the evidence, by
Z\geq\exp\bigl(-{\mathsf{J}}(q^{\star})\bigr).

The terminology
evidence lower bound
is now made clear.
∎

2.4
Bibliography

The paper
[
515
]
provided impetus for
variational Bayes, highlighting the fact that the posterior is found by minimizing

{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)
given in (
2.5
).
Our presentation has focused on finding the posterior as the minimizer of the KL divergence, but there are other functionals that may be considered. For instance, the paper
[
193
]
shows that the posterior is found by minimizing a functional of the same structural form as (
2.3
) based on the
\chi^{2}
divergence (see Definition
11.39
), rather than KL divergence. Papers
[
316
,
235
]
show
how to perform variational inference using the family of Renyi-alpha divergences, which include the KL and
\chi^{2}
divergences as well as the Hellinger metric. In
[
91
]
the Fisher
divergence, based on matching
the score associated with the desired posterior, is used as an objective function
for variational inference. However, whilst there are many divergences that could be used to define Bayes Theorem via optimization over the space of measures, the specific choice of KL divergence in which the measure to be minimized over appears in the first argument, has a special place: amongst all

\mathsf{f}
-divergences, it is the
unique choice for which the objective function does not require knowledge
of the normalization constant
Z

[
120
]
. Generalizations of variational inference, wherein the KL divergence is replaced by a different divergence and the log-likelihood by a different loss function, are explored in
[
286
]
; in particular, other choices can be more robust to model and prior misspecification.

Refer to
[
244
]
for a modern approach to variational inference, with applications to topic models for large collections of documents.
For applications in graphical models see
[
496
]
. The paper
[
69
]
provides an accessible introduction to variational inference, including the implementation of CAVI in exponential family models.
An extension to the mean-field model is to include interactions
[
264
]
.
Natural extensions of Gaussians include the Gaussian mixtures considered in
[
295
]
. The derivation of the mean-field equations for Gaussians was first shown in
[
368
]
. Variational inference for sparsity-promoting Bayesian models
[
95
]
is considered in
[
8
]
; see also
[
306
]
.
Discussion of mean-seeking versus mode-seeking may be found in
[
424
]
;
see Sections 4.2 and 4.3 in particular.
The property of
{\mathsf{G}}
presented in Remark
2.13
is highlighted in

[
68
]
and proved in
[
424
, Section 4.3]
.

In this chapter we have seen that the problem of minimizing KL divergence over its first argument can be reformulated in terms of maximizing the
\mathsf{ELBO}
functional. From a conceptual viewpoint, it is often insightful to derive algorithms and estimation procedures using the minimization of KL divergence as a guiding principle; however in practice it is common to work with the
\mathsf{ELBO},
whose definition requires only a density known up to normalizing constant. The idea of minimizing KL divergence/maximizing
\mathsf{ELBO}
is ubiquitous in machine learning and statistics, and it underpins many methods studied in these notes, including learning the prior to posterior map in Section
4.2
, the variational formulations of data assimilation in Sections
7.1
and
7.2
, variational autoencoders in Section
13.6
, and the expectation–maximization algorithm in Section
15.5
, used to learn priors for data assimilation in Section
8.2
.

The
\mathsf{ELBO}
is a non-convex functional even for simple potentials; see the example in Appendix G of
[
294
]
. Due to this non-convexity, it is often challenging to develop guarantees for variational inference outside restricted settings on the distribution. However, there have been several recent papers studying statistical and algorithmic guarantees for variational inference. We refer to
[
295
]
for analysis using gradient flows and reference to the literature.

Chapter 3
Learning the Prior

In Bayesian inference the prior acts as a form of regularization.
This can be seen explicitly for both the optimization and probabilistic approaches
to inverse problems. First, consider the optimization approach and MAP estimation as described in Subsection
1.2.1
. In that setting the objective function is a sum of two terms: the loss function, which is the negative log-likelihood
-\log\mathsf{l}(y|u)
; and a regularizer, given by the negative logarithm of the prior
\rho
.
Secondly, consider the probabilistic approach, and the variational form of Bayes Theorem as described in Subsection
2.1
, and Remark
2.2
, in particular.
The posterior is shown to be the minimizer of an objective defined over probability densities. This objective is the sum of two terms: the data mismatch term, which is the expectation, with respect to
the putative minimizer
q
, of the loss function; and the
regularizer, which is the KL divergence between the putative minimizer
q
and the prior
\rho.
Choice of prior, or relatedly a regularizer, is thus an essential component of both classical and probabilistic approaches to inverse problems.

The prior should, of course, be chosen to reflect prior knowledge. But defining a prior, or regularizer, can be challenging.
Often modellers make simple parametric choices, based on domain-specific knowledge or intuition, within some class of priors
or regularizers that lead to computational tractability. For example if a Gaussian prior is adopted
then the mean and covariance are selected using domain knowledge. However the abundance of data that is available
in many fields suggests the possibility of employing purely data-driven priors for inversion, a major focus of this chapter.

We start, in Section
3.1
, by discussing Bayesian hierarchical modelling,
where parameters of the prior are learned directly from the data available in the Data Assumption
1.2

used to define the inverse problem itself. Then, in Section
3.2
, we introduce the additional Data Assumption
3.3
, for settings where the prior is defined through samples.
We consider learning the prior measure from data
U:=\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}

drawn from the prior, relating this problem to the unsupervised learning task studied in Chapter
13
.
We initially focus on approximation of the prior by use of the empirical measure (see Definition
12.3
) defined by the data
U
, and study the effect of this empirical approximation of the
prior on the posterior. Then, in Section
3.3
, we continue with different ideas from
unsupervised learning as introduced in Chapter
13
,
representing the prior via a pushforward from a latent space. Such an
approach arises when the prior is learned from data as a transport, or by using
autoencoders. We highlight that this approach may be useful even if the
prior is known analytically, and not just through data; working in a latent space can improve the capability of
some algorithms for inversion. In Section
3.3
we also discuss the effect, on the posterior,
of approximating the prior by using a pushforward. Section
3.4
contains concluding
bibliographic remarks.

Remark 3.1
.

In this chapter we focus on learning parameterized priors and regularizers; we use either the single observation
y

defining the inverse problem (hierarchical Bayes), or the data
U
constituting samples from the prior. In
Chapter
5
we will study settings where we have data
\{y^{(n)},u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}

defined by the joint distribution of
(y,u)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and how to learn regularizers and priors in
that context.
∎

3.1
Bayesian Hierarchical Modelling

Recall the Bayesian inverse problem of finding
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
from
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}

when related by (
1.1
), so that

y=G(u)+\eta.

The Bayesian hierarchical methodology postulates a
parameterized family
of priors,
\rho(u;\theta)
and proposes to learn the pair
(u,\theta)

from the single piece of data
y
given by Data Assumption
1.2
.
Thus, we learn both the unknown
u
,
and
parameters
\theta
that define the prior, from
y
.
To formulate this problem we view the parameterized family of priors
\rho(u;\theta)

as conditional probability densities
\rho(u|\theta)
, put a prior
\rho(\theta)
on parameter

\theta
and construct the prior
\rho(u,\theta)=\rho(u|\theta)\rho(\theta)
on the pair
(u,\theta)
. The prior
\rho(\theta)
, being a prior on the parameter of a prior, is known as a
hyperprior
.
Under appropriate generalization of the assumptions of Theorem
1.3
to the jointly
varying pair
(u,\theta)
, the posterior distribution in this case is given by

(3.1)

\pi(u,\theta)=\frac{1}{Z}\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr)\rho(u,\theta);

we have dropped the explicit dependence of the posterior on
y

for notational convenience.

Example 3.2
.

Consider finding
u
from
y
where

y=u+\eta

and
\eta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\gamma_{\eta}^{2}).
We place a prior on
u|\theta

with form
\mathcal{N}(\theta,\sigma^{2});

if we then assume
\theta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\gamma_{\theta}^{2})
then this specifies a jointly Gaussian prior
\rho(u,\theta)

on the pair
(u,\theta)
.
Under the specified prior
\rho(u,\theta)
,
u=\theta+\xi_{u}
and
\theta=\xi_{\theta}
where
\xi_{u}
and
\xi_{\theta}
are independent centered Gaussians with variances
\sigma^{2}
and

\gamma_{\theta}^{2}
. Thus, under this prior,
u=\xi_{u}+\xi_{\theta}
and hence
has distribution given by
\rho(u):=\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}+\gamma_{\theta}^{2}).

Using these formulae we can identify the mean and covariance of the joint random variable: the mean is
0
and the covariance is

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\sigma^{2}+\gamma_{\theta}^{2}&\gamma_{\theta}^{2}\\
\gamma_{\theta}^{2}&\gamma_{\theta}^{2}\end{array}}}\right).

By (
3.1
) we obtain

\pi(u,\theta)\propto\nu\bigl(y-u\bigr)\rho(u,\theta),

where
\nu
is the density of the Gaussian noise distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,\gamma_{\eta}^{2}).

Since both the likelihood and the prior are Gaussian, the posterior
\pi(u,\theta)
is
also Gaussian, and its mean and covariance can be readily computed.

By integrating out
\theta
we arrive at the posterior for the original non-hierarchical problem

\pi(u)\propto\nu\bigl(y-u\bigr)\rho(u).

In general it is not possible to explicitly integrate out the parameter
\theta
and formulate the problem
in terms of
u
only; the specific structure on
\rho(u,\theta)
allows it in this case. In the general setting
it is necessary to solve the inverse problem for pair
(u,\theta).

∎

Hierarchical Bayesian learning works with the same Data Assumption
1.2

that we employ to define the standard Bayesian inverse problem defined by (
1.1
).
Each different data instance for
y
would give rise to a different choice of the parameter
\theta

(if MAP estimation is used), or distribution over the parameter
\theta

(if a fully Bayesian approach is used), entering the prior for
u
.
Hence we obtain a different prior on
u
for each data instance
y.

In the remainder of this chapter, we work in a different scenario where the form of the prior is learned
from samples of that prior; we thus make a different data assumption.

3.2
Prior via Diracs

In the remainder of this chapter we work under the assumption that desired prior
\rho
is only
known to us through samples:

Data Assumption 3.3
.

We are given independent samples
U:=\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}

from the prior
\rho
on
u
, which is unknown.

To clearly understand the methodology of the remainder of this chapter,
it is important to distinguish between the piece of

observational data
,
y
,
for which we wish to solve the inverse problem defined by (
1.1
),
and the
training data
,
U
,
which we use to learn about the prior.

In this section we discuss a direct empirical approximation of the prior (Subsection
3.2.1
),
using the training data, and the properties of the resulting posterior (Subsection
3.2.2
).
In the following Section
3.3
we look at finding smooth approximations of
the prior, using the same training data.

3.2.1
Empirical Prior

As discussed in the preamble to Chapter
13
,
Data Assumption
3.3
immediately yields an empirical
approximation for
\rho:

(3.3)

\displaystyle\rho^{N}(u)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{u^{(n)}}(u)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta(u-u^{(n)}).

We sometimes refer to
\rho
as the population limit of
\rho^{N}.

We note that
\rho^{N}
is a
random probability measure

and is a random approximation of the population limit
\rho.

Recall the following metric on the space of random probability measures, from Subsection
11.1.6
:

(3.4)

\mathsf{d_{rand}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})=\sup_{|f|_{\infty}\leq 1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho(f)-\rho^{\prime}(f)}}\right)^{2}}}\right]}}\right|^{1/2},

where, for any probability measure
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\mathfrak{p}(f):=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\mathfrak{p}}[f(u)]
.
Theorem
11.34
shows that

\mathsf{d_{rand}}(\rho,\rho^{N})^{2}=\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{N}).

Thus a natural approach to the inverse problem is to simply use
\rho^{N}
as prior in place of
\rho
in
Theorem
1.3
. We now discuss this idea.

3.2.2
Empirical Posterior

Under the assumptions of Bayes Theorem
1.3
, the posterior distribution, given prior
\rho
, is

(3.5)

\pi(u)=\frac{1}{Z}\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr)\rho(u);

we have dropped the explicit dependence of the posterior on
y
for notational convenience,
as
y
plays no role in the current discussions.
With the finite sample approximation of the population limit of the prior, and by
application of an appropriate generalization of Bayes Theorem
1.3
to priors which are sums of Diracs,
we obtain the approximate posterior

(3.6)

\pi^{N}(u)=\frac{1}{Z^{N}}\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr)\rho^{N}(u).

From this it follows that

(3.7a)

\displaystyle\pi^{N}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}w^{(n)}\delta_{u^{(n)}}(u),

(3.7b)

\displaystyle w^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\ell^{(n)}\Big/\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell^{(i)}\Bigr),\quad\ell^{(n)}=\mathsf{l}(u^{(n)}),

where, from (
1.2
) but dropping explicit
y
-dependence,

(3.8)

\mathsf{l}(u)=\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr).

We wish to compare the population limit
\pi
with
\pi^{N}
.
From (
3.4
) we have

(3.9)

\mathsf{d_{rand}}(\pi,\pi^{N})=\sup_{|f|_{\infty}\leq 1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi(f)-\pi^{N}(f)}}\right)^{2}}}\right]}}\right|^{1/2},

where
\pi(f):=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\pi}[f(u)]
and
\pi^{N}(f):=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\pi^{N}}[f(u)]
.
We note, because it will be useful in what follows, that

(3.10)

\sup_{|f|_{\infty}\leq F}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi(f)-\pi^{N}(f)}}\right)^{2}}}\right]}}\right|^{1/2}=F\,\mathsf{d_{rand}}(\pi,\pi^{N}).

Theorem
3.5
uses this metric on random probability
measures to quantify the distance between the true posterior and the approximate posterior resulting from
empirical approximation of the prior, using samples. We work under the following assumption:

Assumption 3.4
.

Likelihood
\mathsf{l}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
defined by (
3.8
) is bounded:
there exists
K\in(0,\infty)
such that
\sup_{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{l}(u)=K.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let Assumption
3.4
hold and let
\pi,\pi^{N}
be the posteriors given by (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) respectively, with
\rho_{N}
given by (
3.3
)
and
\mathsf{l}
by (
3.8
). Then,

(3.11)

\mathsf{d_{rand}}(\pi,\pi^{N})\leq\frac{2K}{Z}\mathsf{d_{rand}}(\rho,\rho^{N}).

Proof.

For the true and approximate posterior, we can write the integrals required to estimate

\mathsf{d_{rand}}(\pi,\pi^{N})
by

\pi(f)=\frac{\rho(\mathsf{l}f)}{\rho(\mathsf{l})},\quad\pi^{N}(f)=\frac{\rho^{N}(\mathsf{l}f)}{\rho^{N}(\mathsf{l})}.

Then we have

\displaystyle\pi(f)-\pi^{N}(f)

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho(\mathsf{l}f)}{\rho(\mathsf{l})}-\frac{\rho^{N}(\mathsf{l}f)}{\rho^{N}(\mathsf{l})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho(\mathsf{l}f)-\rho^{N}(\mathsf{l}f)}{\rho(\mathsf{l})}-\frac{\rho^{N}(\mathsf{l}f)\bigl(\rho(\mathsf{l})-\rho^{N}(\mathsf{l})\bigr)}{\rho(\mathsf{l})\rho^{N}(\mathsf{l})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho(\mathsf{l}f)-\rho^{N}(\mathsf{l}f)}{\rho(\mathsf{l})}-\frac{\pi^{N}(f)\bigl(\rho(\mathsf{l})-\rho^{N}(\mathsf{l})\bigr)}{\rho(\mathsf{l})}.

Using the basic inequality
(a-b)^{2}\leq 2(a^{2}+b^{2})
, (
3.10
), that
|\pi^{N}(f)|^{2}\leq 1
for all
|f|_{\infty}\leq 1
and that
|\mathsf{l}|_{\infty},|\mathsf{l}f|_{\infty}\leq K

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi(f)-\pi^{N}(f)}}\right)^{2}}}\right]}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\rho(\mathsf{l})^{2}}\Biggl(\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl(\rho(\mathsf{l}f)-\rho^{N}(\mathsf{l}f)\bigr)^{2}\Bigr]+\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bigl(\pi^{N}(f)\bigr)^{2}\bigl(\rho(\mathsf{l})-\rho^{N}(\mathsf{l})\bigr)^{2}}}\right]\Biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4K^{2}}{\rho(\mathsf{l})^{2}}\mathsf{d_{rand}}(\rho,\rho^{N})^{2}.

Taking the supremum over test functions on the left-hand side and using that
\rho(\mathsf{l})=Z
gives us the desired result.
∎

We note that so far we did not specify how the samples
u^{(n)}
defining the perturbed prior
\rho^{N}
were generated. In fact, the result above holds for any empirical measure. If
u^{(n)}\sim\rho
are sampled i.i.d., however,
\rho^{N}
is a Monte Carlo approximation of
\rho
. Moreover, we can appeal to convergence results for Monte Carlo to show the convergence rate of
\pi^{N}
to the true posterior
\pi
.

Corollary 3.6
.

Let the assumptions made in Theorem
3.5
hold,
and in addition, assume that Data Assumption
3.3
holds so that

\rho^{N}
is the Monte Carlo estimator (
3.3
) of
\rho
. Then

\mathsf{d_{rand}}(\pi,\pi^{N})\leq\frac{2K}{Z}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}.

Proof.

By Theorem
11.34
,
\mathsf{d_{rand}}(\rho,\rho^{N})^{2}\leq\frac{1}{N}
. Using this in the right-hand side of (
3.11
) gives the desired result.
∎

3.3
Prior via Pushforward

In this section, rather than using an empirical prior, we try to determine a smooth approximation
of the prior. To this end, compare Data Assumption
3.3
to Data Assumption
13.1
in Chapter
13
.
The latter assumption arises in unsupervised learning;
notice that, taking
\Upsilon:=\rho
shows that the two assumptions are identical.
Thus, when the prior is only given to us through Data Assumption
3.3
, we may seek to approximate it using the generative modelling techniques of Chapter
13
.

Theorem
11.9
shows that, under appropriate conditions on
\rho,\zeta
, there exists an exact
pushforward map
g
such that
\rho=g_{\sharp}\zeta.
In fact there are many such maps.
We may use ideas such as autoencoders and
transport maps from Chapter
13
, to find an approximation of a map
g
that pushes forward a given density

\zeta
on latent space
\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}
into the prior.

In the context of inverse problems there are several specific reasons why this pushforward
approach to prior modeling can be useful. Firstly, there are settings where it may be of interest to
generate further samples from the prior itself, in addition to those given in Data Assumption
3.3
,
discussed in Subsection
3.3.1
;
it is then desirable that
\zeta
is easy to sample from. A second setting is one in which the
goal is to solve the inverse problem in the latent space, discussed in Subsection
3.3.2
;
it is then desirable that
\zeta
has a closed form density to enable application of methods such as MAP
estimation and MCMC. The third setting, also requiring a closed form density for

\zeta
and a closed form for the approximation of map
g
, is one in which methods such as MAP
estimation and MCMC are applied to solve the inverse problem in the
original space, discussed in Subsection
3.3.3
.
In the second and third settings, once
\zeta
and an approximation of
g
are identified they can be re-used for different
inverse problems for
u
, or for different instances of the realization
y
used in the inverse problem
under consideration, all provided that the prior remains unchanged.

3.3.1
Additional Prior Samples

Throughout this subsection we assume that
\rho=g_{\sharp}\zeta
and assume that
d_{z}=d
so that
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Using Data Assumption
3.3
, and ideas such as transport and autoencoders in Chapter
13
,
we obtain an approximation to
g
, denoted
g^{\prime}
, and hence obtain an approximation
of
\rho
given by
\rho^{\prime}=g^{\prime}_{\sharp}\zeta
. If
\zeta
is easy to sample and
g^{\prime}
is
straightforward to evaluate, then we may obtain approximate i.i.d. samples from
\rho

by sampling i.i.d. from
\zeta
and applying
g^{\prime}
, thereby obtaining samples from
\rho^{\prime}.

It is natural to ask how to estimate the distance between
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
in terms of some measure of
closeness between
g
and
g^{\prime}.
This turns out to be a naturally answered in the
Wasserstein
-p
distances
defined in Subsection
11.1.3
. Recall that, with specific choice of
\mathsf{d}(u,v)=|u-v|_{p}
, the
p
-norm on
\mathbb{R}^{d},

we have

W_{p}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})\coloneqq\biggl({\rm inf}_{\mathfrak{p}\in\Pi_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|u-v|_{p}^{p}\,\mathfrak{p}(u,v)\,dudv\biggr)^{1/p};

here
\Pi_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the set of couplings
of measures
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Thus if
\mathfrak{p}
is
any
specific coupling of
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
then

W_{p}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})^{p}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|u-v|_{p}^{p}\,\mathfrak{p}(u,v)\,dudv=\mathbb{E}^{(u,v)\sim\mathfrak{p}}|u-v|_{p}^{p}.

To upper-bound the right-hand side, we define a coupling
\mathfrak{p}:=(g,g^{\prime})_{\sharp}\zeta;

clearly the marginals are
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
as required. Furthermore

\mathbb{E}^{(u,v)\sim\mathfrak{p}}|u-v|_{p}^{p}=\mathbb{E}^{z\sim\zeta}|g(z)-g^{\prime}(z)|_{p}^{p}.

We have thus proved:

Theorem 3.7
.

Let
g,g^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be two Borel measurable maps and let

\rho=g_{\sharp}\zeta,\rho^{\prime}=g^{\prime}_{\sharp}\zeta.
Then

W_{p}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})\leq\Bigl(\mathbb{E}^{z\sim\zeta}|g(z)-g^{\prime}(z)|_{p}^{p}\Bigr)^{1/p}.

3.3.2
Posterior in Latent Space

Throughout this subsection we again assume that
\rho=g_{\sharp}\zeta.

We discuss the idea of solving the inverse problem in the latent space
where
\zeta
is defined.
In practice, of course,
g
will only be known approximately. This approximation may be
obtained, as in the preceding subsection, using samples given in Data Assumption
3.3
. But
the methodology in this subsection may also be useful in settings where prior
\rho
is known analytically; this
is because solution of the inverse problem may be easier in a latent space.

We consider the inverse problem of finding
z
from
y
, where

y=G\bigl(g(z)\bigr)+\eta.

Since
g
is a deterministic map Assumption
1.1
can be reinterpreted as:

Assumption 3.8
.

The distribution of the random variable
(z,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
is defined by assuming that
z\perp\!\!\!\!\perp\eta
, that
z\sim\zeta
and that
\eta\sim\nu
, where
\zeta
and
\nu
are probability density functions and, in addition,
\nu
is centered.

Under this assumption we may apply Bayes Theorem
1.3
to define the posterior distribution

(3.12)

\pi_{g}(z)=\frac{1}{Z_{g}}\nu\Bigl(y-G\bigl(g(z)\bigr)\Bigr)\zeta(z).

If map
g
is given by the decoder of an autoencoder, as in Section
13.5
, then typically
the latent space dimension
d_{z}
is smaller than
d
. In the case of transport, as described
in Section
13.2
,
d_{z}=d
and we have the following:

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
d_{z}=d
and assume that
\rho=g_{\sharp}\zeta
and that
g
is invertible
and that the inverse has positive determinant everywhere on
\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Then
\pi_{g}
given by (
3.12
) and the desired posterior
\pi

are related by the identity
\pi=g_{\sharp}\pi_{g}.

Remark 3.10
.

The significance of Proposition
3.9
is that it implies that we can
solve the Bayesian inverse problem (
3.12
), posed in the latent space,
and push forward under

g
to find solutions of the original Bayesian inverse problem defined
by (
3.5
). For example if we generate samples under
\pi_{g}
in the latent space, then application of
g
to those samples will generate samples
under
\pi
in the original space. Hence,
if pair
(g,\zeta)
is known, then the original Bayesian
inverse problem for
\pi
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}

may be converted to one for
\pi_{g}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}
;
pushforward of
\pi_{g}
under
g
yields solution to the original problem. Thus we have designed a method for solving Bayesian inverse problems when prior
\rho
is known only through samples by converting to an inverse problem
for
z
.

We may apply MAP or MCMC methods
because the posterior
\pi_{g}(z)
has an explicit
formula for its density when
G,g
and density for
\zeta
are known analytically.
Furthermore, if the proposal distribution for MCMC requires samples
from
\zeta
then it is desirable that
\zeta
is easy to sample from.
We point out that performing MCMC in latent space can be useful even in cases where the prior
\rho
is fully known and not only accessed through samples, as in some cases the posterior in latent space may be easier to sample from.
A reference that illustrates this idea can be found in the bibliography Section
3.4
.
We also discuss data assimilation in a latent space in Section
8.5
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.9
.

From Lemma
11.45
with
u=g(z)
, and (
11.20
) in particular,

\displaystyle(g_{\sharp}\pi_{g})(u)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\pi_{g}\circ g^{-1}\bigr)(u){\rm det}D\bigl(g^{-1}\bigr)(u)

\displaystyle\propto\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr)\bigl(\zeta\circ g^{-1}\bigr)(u){\rm det}D\bigl(g^{-1}\bigr)(u)

\displaystyle=\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr)g_{\sharp}\zeta(u)

\displaystyle=\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr)\rho(u)

\displaystyle\propto\pi(u).

Thus
(g_{\sharp}\pi_{g})(\cdot)\propto\pi(\cdot)
and since both are probability density functions, they are in fact equal.
∎

Remark
3.10
shows that, if we learn an exact transport map
g
satisfying
\rho=g_{\sharp}\zeta,
then we
can exactly sample the true posterior with prior
\rho
by sampling
the posterior distribution associated with a (potentially simpler) inverse problem with prior
\zeta
,
and applying
g
to those samples. In practice, however,
we learn
g
from
\rho^{N}\approx\rho
and so we recover an approximate transport map
g^{\prime}
; the issue of lack of exactness is further compounded by only learning the transport map over a parametric family of densities and by not iterating the optimization solver to the global minimizer. In Subsection
3.3.3

we prove Theorem
3.11
which addresses the effect of approximate
g^{\prime}
:
we assume that the approximation of the exact transport map leads to an approximate smooth prior, in the space of
u
, and look at the effect on the posterior of the approximation error between this prior and the true prior. In Theorem
3.5
of Subsection
3.2.2
we have already studied a related problem, namely the
effect on the posterior of replacing the prior by an empirical approximation.

3.3.3
Effect of Approximate Pushforward

Throughout this subsection we again assume that
\rho=g_{\sharp}\zeta.
However, as discussed
in Subsection
3.3.1
,
in practice we will not have an
exact
pushforward
g
from
\zeta
to
\rho,
but rather will have
an approximation
g^{\prime}
.
We will thus have an approximate prior
\rho^{\prime}
in place of
\rho.

Consider the inverse problem arising from use of a smooth approximate prior
\rho^{\prime}\approx\rho.

This gives rise to an approximate posterior,
\pi^{\prime}:

(3.13)

\pi^{\prime}(u)=\frac{1}{Z^{\prime}}\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr)\rho^{\prime}(u).

It is thus important to know that a small change in the prior leads
to a small change in the posterior. We prove this in the following
theorem which exhibits conditions under which the prior to posterior
map is Lipschitz in the Hellinger metric. In the proof of the theorem,
and conditions that precede it, we let
\mathsf{l}(u)=\nu\bigl(y-G(u)\bigr)
, suppressing dependence on
y
,
as in Section
3.2
.

Theorem 3.11
.

Let Assumption
3.4
hold. Consider posteriors

\pi,\pi^{\prime}
in (
3.5
), (
3.13
) corresponding, respectively, to priors

\rho,\rho^{\prime}
. Then, we have

(3.14)

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2K}{(\sqrt{Z}+\sqrt{Z^{\prime}})\sqrt{Z^{\prime}}}+\frac{\sqrt{K}}{\sqrt{Z^{\prime}}}}}\right)\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime}).

The preceding theorem, and proof that follows, are readily modified to the setting where the prior distributions
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
are both supported on bounded open set
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d},
in which case the bound on the likelihood in Assumption
3.4

may be replaced by a bound over
D
.

Proof of Theorem
3.11
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho}{Z}}-\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho^{\prime}}{Z^{\prime}}}}}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho}{Z}}-\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho}{Z^{\prime}}}}}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho}{Z^{\prime}}}-\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{l}\rho^{\prime}}{Z^{\prime}}}}}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{Z}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{Z^{\prime}}}}}\right|\sqrt{Z}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\sqrt{Z^{\prime}}}\|\sqrt{\mathsf{l}\rho}-\sqrt{\mathsf{l}\rho^{\prime}}\|_{L^{2}}.

The first term can be rewritten using the identity

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{Z}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{Z^{\prime}}}}}\right|\sqrt{Z}=\frac{|Z-Z^{\prime}|}{(\sqrt{Z}+\sqrt{Z^{\prime}})\sqrt{Z^{\prime}}}.

The
difference between the normalization constants can be bounded, using Lemma

11.2
, by

\displaystyle|Z-Z^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\mathsf{l}(u)\rho(u)-\mathsf{l}(u)\rho^{\prime}(u)|\,du

\displaystyle\leq K\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\rho(u)-\rho^{\prime}(u)|\,du

\displaystyle=2K\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq 2K\sqrt{2}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime}).

For the second term, we have

\|\sqrt{\mathsf{l}\rho}-\sqrt{\mathsf{l}\rho^{\prime}}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\sqrt{K}\|\sqrt{\rho}-\sqrt{\rho^{\prime}}\|_{L^{2}}=\sqrt{2K}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime}).

Collecting the bounds for the two terms, we obtain the result.
∎

3.4
Bibliography

Bayesian hierarchical modeling for inverse problems is reviewed in
[
96
]
. Hierarchical models for level-set inversion were introduced in
[
155
]
while hierarchical models to promote sparsity in the reconstructions are studied in
[
95
,
278
,
500
,
426
]
.
The idea of learning prior probabilistic models from data is
overviewed in
[
33
]
. An application is
described in
[
376
]
where a generative adversarial
network (GAN) is used to determine a mapping from a Gaussian
to the space of prior data samples. The paper
[
188
]
also implicitly learns
a prior, through simultaneous consideration of multiple inverse problems; but it also learns the
posterior distribution for each of these inverse problems at the same time, linking to
the two subsequent Chapters
4
and
5
.
Many methods of this type
reduce the dimension of the unknown parameter space, determining
a latent space of low effective dimension. A different approach to learning this mapping is to use invertible maps

[
283
]
and the work on
normalizing flows
[
464
,
445
]
;
see
[
33
]
for application of invertible
maps to prior construction for inversion.
This idea can be combined with variational inference to solve
sampling problems in general, and inverse problems in particular

[
411
,
458
,
184
,
367
]
.
The use of triangular transport maps for Bayesian inference was introduced in
[
158
]
. The paper
[
202
]
considers transforming a sparsity promoting hierarchical prior into a standard Gaussian prior with the goal of obtaining a posterior distribution in latent space that is easier to sample from.

The idea of learning regularizers from data, to define objective functions
for the optimization approach to inversion, is overviewed in

[
30
]
and
[
59
]
. The paper
[
444
]
provides
a framework for the subject which employs structured factorizations of
data matrices to learn semidefinite regularizers.
The paper
[
325
]
develops an adversarial approach
to the problem. Inspired by the Neumann series, the work
[
201
]
proposes an end-to-end learning approach that utilizes data-driven nonlinear regularizers. Score-based generative priors for inverse problems in imaging are considered, for instance, in
[
167
,
459
]
.

A collection of stability results relating the posterior error to perturbations in the prior measure with respect to various metrics and divergences (e.g., KL divergence,
\chi^{2}
divergence and Wasserstein-1 metric) can be found in
[
189
,
447
]
. We refer to Chapter
11
for background on these and other distances and divergences. While the stability results presented in this chapter are with respect to distance between two priors, the results can also be translated to stability with
respect to the distance between the transport maps that define the prior. A set of these stability results for various metrics and divergences can be found in
[
48
]
.

The field of image-processing has developed a range of machine-learned prior modeling or regularization techniques,
known collectively as “Plug-and-Play” methods
[
415
,
271
]
. The original motivation is to use image
de-noisers, trained on paired data that is independent of the observations defining the inverse problem, to learn the effect of regularization in an iterative optimization technique that alternates between minimizing the loss and applying the regularizer.
These ideas are generalized to Bayesian inversion, rather than MAP estimation, in
[
513
]
, where the denoising
step is often undertaken with a diffusion-based generative model
[
445
]
. The idea of using diffusion-based
generative modeling to solve Bayesian inverse problems first appears in
[
127
]
; recent work in this area
may be found in
[
517
]
. Benchmark problems for diffusion-based “Plug and Play” methods are proposed in

[
520
]
. In addition to prior modeling based on diffusions, similar ideas are being developed using flow-matching techniques
[
318
,
519
]
.

Chapter 4
Transport to the Posterior

In this chapter we introduce various transport approaches to determining the posterior distribution. Recalling the Definition
11.8
of a transport
map, we build on the transport methodologies introduced in Chapter
13
. Section
4.1
is devoted to the question of whether a transport map, taking prior to posterior under pushforward, exists.
In Section
4.2
we formulate the problem of mapping the prior to the posterior
as an optimization problem over a parameterized class of transports. We discuss a population formulation
of the objective function, as well as the empirical approximation implemented in practice.
Furthermore, we demonstrate that the methodology is a special
case of variational inference and we obtain insights into the approach by considering a nonparametric
formulation. Section
4.3

generalizes the approach beyond variational inference, using an objective function
defined by divergences other than KL, leading to a methodology which is
usable given a prior which is known only through samples.
In Section
4.4
we generalize the transport approach: we consider maps from general latent spaces, not restricted to the prior, to the posterior. Section
4.5
is devoted to using transport to enhance
Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC).
Bibliographic remarks are contained in Section
4.6
.

4.1
Existence of the Prior-to-Posterior Map

Consider Bayes Theorem
1.3

written as map relating prior
\rho

to posterior
\pi
via the relation

(4.1a)

\displaystyle\pi(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z}\mathsf{l}(u)\rho(u),

(4.1b)

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}\,\bigl[\mathsf{l}(u)\bigr].

We drop explicit dependence on
y
since we do not exploit it in this chapter.
Equation (
4.1
) defines a nonlinear map
\rho\mapsto\pi
on the space of probability density functions,
as discussed in Remark
1.4
.
We now ask whether we can realize this map via a transport map
T
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with the property
\pi=T_{\sharp}\rho
. We say that map
T
with this property is a
prior-to-posterior transport map.
Furthermore, if
T
is invertible then we may write
\rho=(T^{-1})_{\sharp}\pi
. Although
optimal

transport will not play a significant role in the methods
developed in this chapter, it can be used to provide
a straightforward existence theorem for the desired transport maps:

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\rho\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})

be a probability density function.
Assume that

\mathbb{E}^{z\sim\rho}|z|_{r}^{r}<\infty
for some
r\in(1,\infty)
, that
\mathsf{l}(\cdot)

satisfies Assumption
3.4
and that
Z
given by (
4.1
b)
is strictly positive. Then there is a
Borel measurable map
T
with property that
\pi=T_{\sharp}\rho.

Proof.

The result is a direct corollary of Theorem
11.9
if we prove that
\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\pi}|u|_{r}^{r}<\infty.

This follows from noting that

\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\pi}|u|_{r}^{r}\leq\frac{K}{Z}\mathbb{E}^{z\sim\rho}|z|_{r}^{r}<\infty.

∎

Remark 4.2
.

The optimal transport map
T
, whose existence is asserted in the preceding theorem,
is unique within the Monge class defined by Theorem
11.9
. However, it
is not the unique map from
\rho
to
\pi.
Indeed the methodology we develop in this
chapter will not invoke optimality in the sense of optimal transport maps.
∎

4.2
Learning the Prior-to-Posterior Map

In practice an exact prior-to-posterior transport must be approximated, and in this and the following sections we do so within a class of parameterized maps
\mathcal{T}_{\Theta},
where
\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
denotes the parameter space.
In Subsection
4.2.1
we consider the idealized setting of infinite data, and describe how to find an optimal value of the parameter
\theta\in\Theta
by optimizing a population-based objective function. Subsection
4.2.2
builds on this idealized setting to define the empirical objective function used in practice.
In Subsection
4.2.3
we quantify the effect of using an approximate transport map on the posterior
approximation and in Subsection
4.2.4
we show that the methodology is a special case of variational inference.

4.2.1
Population Objective

In this subsection we assume that an explicit formula for
\rho
is available

and
that we can evaluate expectations under
\rho
exactly; we
define an optimization problem for
\theta
under these two assumptions. In
the following subsection we relax the second assumption, and instead evaluate
expectations approximately using samples from
\rho
and Monte Carlo integration.
As in Section
13.2
, we work with function classes
\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}
that satisfy the inclusion

(4.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}\subset\Bigl\{T(\cdot\,;\theta):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},\,\theta\in\Theta\Big|\,\,&T(\cdot\,;\theta)\text{ is a }C^{1}\text{diffeomorphism},\\
&\det D_{u}T(u;\theta)>0,\quad\forall u\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\forall\theta\in\Theta\Bigr\}.\end{split}

We aim to find an element
T^{\star}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}
such that,
typically only approximately,
T^{\star}_{\sharp}\rho=\pi.

Example 4.3
.

Examples of classes of smooth invertible functions
T(\cdot\,;\theta):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
are discussed in Section
13.3

where normalizing flows are introduced.
In particular neural ODEs,
introduced in Subsection
13.3.2
provide an example of
\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}.

We first introduce a family of neural networks

h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
– see Remark
12.6
,
and the material preceding it. We then consider the ODE

(4.3)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{dv}{dt}&=h(v;\theta),\\
v(0)&=u.\end{split}

Noting that
v(t)
depends on
(u,\theta)
we set

T(u;\theta)=v(1).
This defines class
\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}.
Crucially, the discussion in Subsection
13.3.2
demonstrates
how the inverse of
T(\cdot;\theta)
can also be calculated via a differential equation, and shows how the
determinant of the Jacobian of
T(\cdot\,;\theta)
can be evaluated.
∎

Using the KL divergence to measure discrepancy
between the pushforward of the prior and the posterior
we define our goal as being determination of parameter
\theta

to make the following objective function small:

(4.4)

\mathsf{F}(\theta)=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(T(\cdot\,;\theta)_{\sharp}\rho\|\pi\bigr).

By Theorem
4.1
we know that there exists map
g^{\star}
such that

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(g^{\star}_{\sharp}\rho\|\pi)=0.
Thus minimizing
\mathsf{F}(\cdot)
forms a natural
objective. Note, however, that Theorem
4.1
asserts existence of

g^{\star}
by use of optimal transport. In contrast the objective function (
4.4
)
does not appeal to optimal transport in any way; there is hence no reason to expect an optimizer,
found by minimizing over
\theta
, to be close to an
optimal
transport.

By Theorem
11.46
, which ensures the invariance of KL divergence under invertible and differentiable transformations such as those in the class
\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}
, minimizing (
4.4
) is equivalent to minimizing

(4.5)

\mathsf{F}(\theta)=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\rho\|T^{-1}(\cdot\,;\theta)_{\sharp}\pi\bigr).

This is exactly the same problem considered in Section
13.2
, with

g\mapsto T^{-1}
,
\zeta\mapsto\pi
and

\Upsilon\mapsto\rho
.
1
1

1

See Remark
13.6
.
Thus, since
\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}\log\rho(u)
is
independent of
\theta
, or directly from (
13.6
), we deduce that minimizing
\mathsf{F}(\cdot)

is equivalent to minimizing

-\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}\Bigl[\log\pi\circ T(u;\theta)+\log{\rm det}D_{u}T(u;\theta)\Bigr].

Using the expression (
4.1
) for the posterior in terms of
the prior, and noting that
\log Z
is an additive constant with respect to
\theta
,
we may define the optimization problem

(4.6a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}\Bigl[\log\rho\circ T(u;\theta)+\log\mathsf{l}\circ T(u;\theta)+\log{\rm det}D_{u}T(u;\theta)\Bigr],

(4.6b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).

Once we have (approximately) solved this problem we may employ transport map
T^{\star}=T(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})

to approximately map the prior to the posterior:
\pi\approx T^{\star}_{\sharp}\rho
.

Remark 4.4
.

We observe that, as for the Metropolis-Hastings MCMC of
Section
15.6
, it is not necessary to know the normalization constant
for the posterior in order to apply the methodology of this section. This may be seen from Remark
2.2
after
noting that the transport map methodology for Bayesian inversion is simply a specific instance of variational
inference; we show this latter fact in Subsection
4.2.4
.

The methodology in this subsection
is not specific to inverse problems; rather it applies to the sampling of
any measure
\pi
which is defined via (
4.1
). The methodology requires knowledge
of
\mathsf{l}(\cdot)
and reference measure
\rho(\cdot)
. No data point
y
is explicitly required. If, however, the density
\mathsf{l}(u)
is defined
by a Bayesian inverse problem, then Data Assumption
1.2
is needed to provide the single data
point
y
entering the likelihood.
∎

4.2.2
Empirical Objective

To implement the optimization problem defined by (
4.6
) it is necessary to empiricalize the objective function, in order to
avoid evaluating expectations over
\rho
. To this end, here we work under the following generalization of Data Assumption
3.3
:

Data Assumption 4.5
.

We are able to evaluate prior
\rho(u)
and likelihood
\mathsf{l}(u)
for all
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

We are given samples
U:=\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
drawn i.i.d. from
\rho
.

Using the
training data

U
from
\rho
we define

(4.7)

\displaystyle\rho^{N}(u)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{u^{(n)}}(u).

We may then define the following minimization problem:

(4.8a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta)

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho^{N}}\Bigl[\log\rho\circ T(u;\theta)+\log\mathsf{l}\circ T(u;\theta)+\log{\rm det}D_{u}T(u;\theta)\Bigr],

(4.8b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta).

Note that we use Data Assumption
4.5
to define this objective function:
samples from
\rho
are used to compute expectation under
\rho^{N}
; and the ability to evaluate prior and likelihood are used to compute the terms
\log\rho\circ T(u;\theta)
and
\log\mathsf{l}\circ T(u;\theta).

Once we have (approximately) solved the minimization problem (
4.8
)
we may employ transport map
T^{\star}=T(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})

to (approximately) map the prior to the posterior:
\pi\approx T^{\star}_{\sharp}\rho
.

4.2.3
Effect of Transport Error on the Posterior

In the preceding subsection we explain how to identify map
T^{\star}
that approximates an exact transport map
T
,
leading to an approximate posterior
\pi^{\star}=T^{\star}_{\sharp}\rho
.
It is natural to ask what effect the error in approximating
T
has on the posterior.
By using identical ideas to those leading to Theorem
3.7
we may prove:

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
T,T^{\star}
be two Borel measurable maps on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let

\pi=T_{\sharp}\rho,\pi^{\star}=T^{\star}_{\sharp}\rho
. Then

W_{p}(\pi,\pi^{\star})\leq\Bigl(\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}|T(u)-T^{\star}(u)|_{p}^{p}\Bigr)^{1/p}.

4.2.4
Connection to Variational Inference

In this subsection we connect the learning of a transport
from prior to posterior, to a variational inference
problem of the type introduced in Chapter
2
.
We work in the population loss setting of Subsection
4.2.1
. Consider a parametric class of maps

\mathcal{T}
satisfying (
4.2
). We drop explicit
\Theta

dependence as we will now formulate optimization over the class
\mathcal{T}

itself, rather than over the parameters defining the class.
Given
\mathcal{T}
we may define the variational class
\mathcal{Q}:=\{q:q=T_{\sharp}\rho,T\in\mathcal{T}\}
. And
given the solution
\theta^{\star}
defined by the prior-to-posterior transport
optimization problem (
4.6
) we may define
q^{\star}\coloneqq T(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})_{\sharp}\rho
.

Theorem 4.7
.

Probability density
q^{\star}\in{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

solves the variational inference problem

q^{\star}\in\argmin_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|\pi).

Proof.

We first note that the class
\mathcal{T}
necessarily comprises only invertible maps. We showed above that solving the optimization problem (
4.6b
),
with
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)
defined by (
4.6a
), is equivalent to minimizing
\mathsf{F}(\cdot)
from (
4.5
).
But from Theorem
11.46
we have that, for invertible and differentiable transformations,

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\rho\|T^{-1}(\cdot\,;\theta)_{\sharp}\pi)=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(T(\cdot\,;\theta)_{\sharp}\rho\|\pi).

Thus,

\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)=\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(T(\cdot\,;\theta)_{\sharp}\rho\|\pi).

Lastly,
T(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a differentiable map, and its derivative has positive determinant; it is therefore an invertible map as a consequence of being an element of
a parametric class of maps
\mathcal{T}
satisfying (
4.2
).
Thus
q^{\star}=T(\cdot;\theta^{\star})_{\sharp}\rho\in\mathcal{Q}
.
∎

Example 4.8
.

Consider the setting where the prior is a standard Gaussian
\mathcal{N}(0,I).

Now we assume that
T
is affine. Specifically, we let

\Theta:=\Bigl\{\theta:=(m,A)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\Big|\det(A)>0\Bigr\}

and set

(4.9)

\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}:=\Bigl\{T(u;\theta)=Au+m\,\Big|(m,A)\in\Theta\quad\theta=(m,A)\in\Theta\Bigr\}.

Then, for
T\in\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}
it holds that
D_{u}T(u;\theta)=A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is an invertible matrix with positive determinant. An affine transformation of a standard Gaussian yields a Gaussian random variable. In fact, the class of approximate posterior distributions then consists of multivariate Gaussians of the form

\mathcal{Q}=\Bigl\{q=\mathcal{N}(m,AA^{\top})\Big|\;m\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\,\,A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},\,\,\det(A)>0\Bigr\}.

Hence, solving the optimization problem for
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)
is a subclass of the general Gaussian variational inference problem considered in Subsection
2.2.2
. Here we
are parameterizing the Gaussian covariance
through a square root. It is possible to parameterize the covariance using its Cholesky factorization by
further restricting
A
to the class of lower triangular matrices

A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with positive diagonal entries.
∎

Example 4.9
.

Assume that prior
\rho
is a probability density function in the mean-field family introduced in Definition
2.3
:

\rho(u)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\rho_{i}(u_{i}).

Here each
\rho_{i}
is a probability distribution over
\mathbb{R}.

We now seek probability distribution
q
over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
in the same form:

q(u)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}q_{i}(u_{i}).

Again each
q_{i}
is a one-dimensional probability distribution over
\mathbb{R}.

We seek each of these
q_{i}
as the pushforward of
\rho_{i}
, under map
T_{0}(\cdot\,;\theta_{i}):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
for some judicious choice of parameter
\theta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}.
To this end we define

(4.10)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}&:=\Bigl\{T(u;\theta)=\bigl(T_{0}(u_{1};\theta_{1}),\dots,T_{0}(u_{d};\theta_{d})\bigr),\theta=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times d},\\
&\qquad T_{0}(\cdot\,;\theta_{0})\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})\,\,\textrm{diffeomorphism,}\\
&\qquad\det D_{u}T_{0}(u;\theta)>0,\,\forall(u,\theta_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}\Bigr\}.\end{split}

Then, the corresponding variational class is

\mathcal{Q}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{q=T(\cdot\,;\theta)_{\sharp}\rho,\quad T(\cdot\,;\theta)\in\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}}}\right\}.

∎

4.3
Sample-Based Objective Functions

Recall that the method described in Subsection
4.2.2
requires knowledge of the density
\rho
, as well
as samples from it; in particular an explicit
expression for
\log\rho
is required. We now describe an alternative approach, which is purely sample-based;
to achieve this we use the energy distance introduced in Definition
11.27
, rather than KL divergence, to define an objective
function. In doing so we avoid the need to have a formula for the prior:
all that is required is samples. This is similar to the situation in
Section
3.2
where we constructed an approximate prior from samples.
We start, however, with a population level objective function, defined through the energy distance:

(4.11)

\mathsf{F}(\theta)=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm E}}}^{2}\bigl(T(\cdot\,;\theta)_{\sharp}\rho\|\pi\bigr).

We show that it is possible to empirically approximate this objective
function under the following generalization of
Data Assumption
3.3
:

Data Assumption 4.10
.

We are given samples
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
assumed
to be drawn i.i.d. from prior measure
\rho
on
u
which is unknown.
We are able to evaluate likelihood
\mathsf{l}(u)
for any
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Recall
\rho^{N}
defined by (
4.7
), repeated here for expository purposes:

\displaystyle\rho^{N}(u)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{u^{(n)}}(u).

We note that this measure is available to us
under Data Assumption
4.10
.
From this measure we may define

\displaystyle\pi^{N}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z^{N}}\mathsf{l}(u)\rho^{N}(u),

\displaystyle Z^{N}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho^{N}}\,\bigl[\mathsf{l}(u)\bigr].

Since
\rho^{N}
is an empirical measure, constructed from
samples from
\rho
, we may explicitly construct
\pi^{N}
by evaluating the likelihood

\mathsf{l}(\cdot)
at the samples and, by renormalizing, define weights that lie on the
probability simplex – see equation (
3.7
). In the notation prevailing here this
yields

\displaystyle\pi^{N}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}w^{(n)}\delta_{u^{(n)}}(u),

\displaystyle w^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\ell^{(n)}\Big/\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell^{(i)}\Bigr),\quad\ell^{(n)}=\mathsf{l}(u^{(n)}).

We may, then, approximate (
4.11
) empirically, under
Data Assumption
4.10
, by

\mathsf{F}^{N}(\theta)=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm E}}}^{2}\bigl(T(\cdot\,;\theta)_{\sharp}\rho^{N},\pi^{N}\bigr).

Following the ideas outlined in Remark
11.29
we then obtain the approximation

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm E}}}^{2}\bigl(T(\cdot\,;\theta)_{\sharp}\rho\|\pi\bigr)\approx\frac{2}{NM}\sum_{i,j}|T(u^{(i)};\theta)-v^{(j)}|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i,j}|T(u^{(i)};\theta)-T(u^{(j)};\theta)|-\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{i,j}|v^{(i)}-v^{(j)}|,

where the
\{v^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{M}
are drawn i.i.d. from
\pi^{N}
by resampling.

Remark 4.11
.

The ideas in this section are readily generalized to the use of the maximum mean discrepancy

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MMD}}}
from Definition
11.20
, with other choices of kernel, rather than the energy
distance specifically. Furthermore, the discussion in Remark
11.78
is relevant to
understanding the different choices of objective function
that we use throughout this book.
∎

4.4
Learning Other Maps to the Posterior

In the preceding two subsections we sought a pushforward map from prior to posterior. This is advantageous when we would like the posterior to inherit certain properties of the prior. For example, if the prior and posterior have the same tail behavior, then the transport map
T
only needs to depart from an identity map in the bulk of the distribution. Similarly, if the posterior and prior only differ on a low-dimensional subspace of the parameters
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d},
then the map
T
can be represented using a
ridge function
that is constant for inputs orthogonal to the subspace of interest. Pushing forward from the prior can also be advantageous because priors that are easily sampled are often deployed in practice. However
in some settings it may be computationally expedient to seek
\pi

which is the pushforward of a different, easy-to-sample, reference density
\varrho(u)
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, (such as a multivariate Gaussian) rather than the prior
\rho(u)
(when it is not Gaussian). Choosing a different reference measure may thus be convenient when it is challenging to sample from the prior density or when the posterior is very different from the prior – for example having different tail behavior.

To consider this setting we replace (
4.5
) by

(4.12)

\mathsf{F}(\theta)=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\varrho\|T^{-1}(\cdot\,;\theta)_{\sharp}\pi\bigr).

Similar analysis to that leading to (
4.6a
) in the case of mapping the prior to the
posterior shows that we may learn the parameters
\theta\in\Theta
solving (
4.12
) by minimizing

(4.13)

{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)=-\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\varrho}\Bigl[\log\rho\circ T(u;\theta)+\log\mathsf{l}\circ T(u;\theta)+\log{\rm det}D_{u}T(u;\theta)\Bigr].

Letting
\theta^{\star}
denote the optimal parameters, the resulting posterior approximation is given by
\pi\approx T(\cdot;\theta^{\star})_{\sharp}\varrho
.
Again, as in Subsection
4.2
, the expectation
over the reference density must be evaluated empirically and, for this, we generalize Data Assumption
4.5
as follows:

Data Assumption 4.12
.

We are able to evaluate prior
\rho(u)
and likelihood
\mathsf{l}(u)
for all
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

We are given samples
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
drawn i.i.d. from
\varrho
.

Remark 4.13
.

Unlike the variational inference problem in Chapter
2
, we now have two degrees of freedom: the reference measure and the map. In principle, one may even parameterize the reference measure
\varrho(\cdot,\vartheta)
and learn its parameters
\vartheta
simultaneously with learning the map
T(\cdot\,;\theta)
. For example, we might parameterize
\varrho
using a Gaussian mixture with unknown means, covariances, and mixture weights.
∎

4.5
Transport-Assisted MCMC

Given a reference distribution
\varrho
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and parameter space
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p},
the approach considered in the previous Section
4.4
yields the posterior approximation
\pi\approx T(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})_{\sharp}\varrho.
This approximation can naturally be used to produce
N
independent approximate posterior samples as follows:

1.

Draw
z^{(n)}\overset{\rm i.i.d.}{\sim}\varrho
for
1\leq n\leq N.

2.

Set
u^{(n)}:=T(z^{(n)};\theta^{\star})
for
1\leq n\leq N.

The extent to which the samples
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
are approximately distributed like the posterior
\pi
is fully determined by the quality of the approximation
\pi\approx T(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})_{\sharp}\varrho.

This section describes a MCMC transport-assisted approach for asymptotically-exact posterior sampling; we refer to Section
15.6
for background on MCMC, a general methodology to obtain approximate and correlated samples from a desired target distribution. To introduce the idea of transport-assisted MCMC recall first that since the map
T(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})
from Section
4.4
is invertible, we also have the approximation
\varrho\approx T^{-1}(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})_{\sharp}\pi.

The idea behind transport-assisted MCMC is that, since
\varrho
is straightforward to sample from, then its approximation
T^{-1}(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})_{\sharp}\pi
will also be straightforward to sample from using MCMC. For example, if
\varrho
is a standard multivariate Gaussian,
and if
T^{-1}(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})_{\sharp}\pi
approximates this measure well, then sampling it using MCMC will be straightforward. Moreover, the reference distribution
\varrho
can be used to design efficient proposal distributions for the MCMC algorithm.

Algorithm 2

Transport-assisted MCMC

1:

Input
: Target distribution
\pi
, Number of samples
N.

2:

Define preconditioned target distribution
T^{-1}(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})_{\sharp}\pi.

3:

Run MCMC to obtain
N
approximate samples
\{z^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
from
T^{-1}(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})_{\sharp}\pi.

4:

Set
u^{(n)}:=T(z^{(n)};\theta^{\star})
for
1\leq n\leq N.

Applying this approach leads to the methodology in Algorithm
2
to generate approximate and correlated posterior samples.
In comparison with directly running MCMC with the target
\pi,
transport-assisted MCMC has the advantage that MCMC algorithms with target
T^{-1}(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})_{\sharp}\pi
may converge faster. In other words, the transport map is used to

precondition
the posterior for MCMC sampling; for instance map
T^{-1}(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})
may pushforward multimodal posterior
\pi
into an approximately Gaussian distribution that is easy to sample from.

Remark 4.14
.

It is important to notice that the extent to which transport-assisted MCMC samples
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
are approximately distributed like the posterior is determined by the convergence of the Markov chain
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
to its limit distribution
T^{-1}(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})_{\sharp}\pi
, rather than by the quality of the approximation
\pi\approx T(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})_{\sharp}\varrho.
In other words, provided that we run MCMC with target
T^{-1}(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})_{\sharp}\pi
for long enough, transport-assisted MCMC will yield samples whose distribution is arbitrarily close to the posterior, even if the approximation
\pi\approx T(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})_{\sharp}\varrho
is poor. A good choice of
\theta^{\star}
will be manifest in fast convergence of the
transport-assisted MCMC.
∎

4.6
Bibliography

The approach to seek prior-to-posterior transport maps
was proposed in
[
158
]
. In particular that paper parameterized
T
as a monotone-triangular map known as the Knothe–Rosenblatt rearrangement
[
486
]
; the advantage of this choice of map is that the determinant Jacobian, required to define the
minimization, is easy to evaluate. The approach was extended to compositions of triangular maps, as used in normalizing flows, in
[
411
]
