. An approach to seek prior-to-posterior maps that are optimal with respect to minimizing a transportation distance (see Subsection
11.1.3
for a discussion on optimal transport) was proposed in
[
465
]
. Transport from a latent random variable to the posterior, within the context of variational Bayes, is used to solve computational imaging problems in
[
458
]
.

An alternative to seeking the transport map directly is to construct the map incrementally by learning a map that is a small perturbation of the identity and that minimizes the distance between the current approximation and the actual posterior distribution. Stein variational gradient descent is one algorithm for finding such maps; it proceeds by seeking the map in a reproducing kernel Hilbert space in which the distance is measured by the KL divergence
[
320
]
. Another approach learns the dynamics of a (possibly stochastic) differential equation to define the transport from its flow map as in
[
277
,
110
,
14
]
.

To handle certain classes of target distributions, it is sometimes desirable to consider objectives other than the KL divergence; examples include use of
\alpha
-divergences
[
235
]
, Wasserstein distances (see Subsection
11.1.3
)
[
17
]
or the development of tailored transport map approximations for heavy-tailed posteriors
[
317
]
.

The development of transport-assisted MCMC sampling algorithms is an active research area, see for example
[
245
,
381
,
511
,
336
,
219
,
26
,
211
,
185
,
374
]
. We refer to
[
80
]
for an overview of transport-based techniques for enhanced MCMC sampling. While some methods first learn the transport map and then apply MCMC in the reference space as described in Section
4.5
, other approaches learn the transport map on the fly using the output of a Markov chain.
These techniques are closely related to
adaptive MCMC
algorithms where the proposal kernel is suitably parameterized and learned online
[
34
,
413
]
.
Transports learned from samples of a target posterior distribution have also been used to learn proposal distributions for importance sampling
[
354
]
.

Chapter 5
Data Dependence of the Posterior

Our focus in this chapter is on learning the dependence of the posterior distribution
\pi^{y}(u)
, for parameter
u
, on the observation
y
.
Recall that, in the setting of Chapter
1
, the posterior is given by

(5.1a)

\displaystyle\pi^{y}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z^{y}}\mathsf{l}(y|u)\rho(u),

(5.1b)

\displaystyle Z^{y}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}\,\bigl[\mathsf{l}(y|u)\bigr].

Here, in contrast to the previous chapter, we retain explicit
y
-dependence in the notation
\pi^{y}
and
Z^{y}
, since our goal is to learn
parametric dependence of
\pi^{y}
on
y
. The resulting learned model can then be repeatedly used for different realizations of
y
, rather than having to relearn a different approximate posterior for each
y
. This reduces the cost of multiple inference procedures to training a single model. Hence, the approach is known as
amortized inference
or simply
amortization
.
In this chapter we consider amortized variational inference, amortized MAP and posterior mean estimation, learning regularizers, and learning priors. The unifying theme is to exploit knowledge about the
y
-dependence of the inverse problem. A similar approach will be applied to learn the
y
-dependence of data assimilation problems in Chapter
10
.

It is helpful to recall the notation, from Remark
1.5
, for the probability density function
\gamma(y,u)
characterizing the joint distribution of the observations and parameter random variable
(y,u)
, and the marginal
\kappa(y)=\int\gamma(y,u)\,du
on the observations.
1
1

1

Note that
Z^{y}=\kappa(y).
The density may be factorized as
\gamma(y,u)=\mathsf{l}(y|u)\rho(u)
, formed from the product of the likelihood
\mathsf{l}(y|u)
, for the distribution on
y|u
, and the prior density
\rho(u)
on parameter
u
. It may also be factorized as
\pi^{y}(u)\kappa(y).

In Section
5.1
we start by discussing the concept of amortization,
with respect to observation
dependence, in the context of MAP and posterior mean estimators.
Section
5.2
studies amortization in the context of variational inference, and includes the use of
prior-to-posterior transport as a running example.
Section
5.3
presents a framework for learning of likelihood models on the basis of paired parameter and observation data;
it may be viewed as a probabilistic generalization of Section
1.6
where we discuss
the learning of forward models for the purpose of inference.
To understand the proposed methodology introduced in these three sections,
it is important to distinguish between the one piece of data
y

for which we wish to solve the inverse problem defined by
(
1.1
) and the
training data

\{y^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}

which we assume is available here. Furthermore, it is important to notice that
we make a variety of assumptions, of increasing complexity, about the
information available to us, in addition to the training data, as we progress through the
subsections comprising Sections
5.1
,
5.2
and
5.3
. The
reader will benefit from comparing Data Assumptions
5.2
,
5.4
,
5.8
and
5.12
before reading Sections
5.1
–
5.3
in detail.

Everything in Sections
5.1
and
5.2
is concerned with learning
about data dependence of the posterior, and amortizing the cost of learning by doing so.
Section
5.3
uses similar ideas to learn the likelihood.
But it is also possible to use modified versions of Data Assumptions
5.8
and
5.12

to learn about regularizers and prior models, respectively. We have already
discussed learning regularizers and prior models in Chapter
3
assuming
the availability of data samples from the prior; here, we learn regularizers and
priors by additionally assuming access to
y
-training data.
In Section
5.4
we study the learning of regularizers for
MAP estimation and learning of priors for general
Bayesian inversion. The goal is to learn regularizers and priors
that perform well, on average, for inversion with data
y\sim\kappa
.
Thus our goal in Section
5.4
is not amortization with respect to
y
-dependence,
the goal of the preceding sections. But since the data assumptions employed overlap,
the learning of regularizers and priors is included in this chapter.
We end with Section
5.5
containing bibliographic remarks.

5.1
Amortized Point Estimators

In this section we discuss amortization of point estimators, in particular, the MAP estimator
and the posterior mean.
Theorem
1.3
delivers the posterior given in (
5.1
).
The MAP estimator of
u
given data
y
is defined, in Subsection
1.2.1
,
as any point solving the maximization problem

u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}(y)\in\argmax_{{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\pi^{{y}}({u}).

The posterior mean estimator of
u
given data
y
is defined, in Subsection
1.2.2
, as

u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(y)=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{u\sim\pi^{y}}[u].

The MAP estimator and posterior mean estimator coincide for Gaussian
\pi^{{y}}
, but are otherwise typically distinct point estimators.

In this chapter we emphasize that
u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}
and
u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}
are functions of
y
: we write
u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and denote
the MAP point at a given observation
y
as
u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}(y)
, and similarly write
u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(y)
.
We discuss two approaches to learn the dependence of the MAP estimator on data; that is, to amortize MAP estimation. In addition, we describe one way to amortize posterior mean estimation.
To this end, we introduce the parameter set
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
and let
\mathcal{R}_{\Theta}\subset C(\mathbb{R}^{k},\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote
a parameterized set of functions
u(\cdot\,;\theta)
for
\theta\in\Theta.

We seek a function
u\in\mathcal{R}_{\Theta}
to approximate
the dependence of the MAP or posterior mean estimator on data
y
.

Example 5.1
.

We might take
\mathcal{R}_{\Theta}
to comprise a family of neural networks, with specified depths and widths, with
\Theta
comprising the parameter space for the weights and biases
which define a specific element in
\mathcal{R}_{\Theta}.
See Remark
12.6
, and the material preceding it,
for discussion of neural networks mapping Euclidean spaces into one another.
∎

In Subsection
5.1.1
we make Data Assumption
5.2
: we assume that we have training data comprising
only observation realizations, and learn an amortized MAP estimator that minimizes the standard MAP objective from Chapter

2
in expectation over the observations.
In Subsection
5.1.2
we employ Data Assumption
5.4
: we assume that we have pairs of observations and numerically computed MAP estimates, and treat the problem of finding an amortized MAP estimator as a supervised learning problem. In Subsection
5.1.3
we study posterior mean estimation,
using Data Assumption
5.8
: we assume we have samples from the joint distribution on parameters and observations.

5.1.1
Amortized MAP Estimation Using Only Observations

We start by discussing an approach that uses only the observations to learn an amortized MAP estimator. We make the following data assumption:

Data Assumption 5.2
.

We are able to evaluate the prior
\rho(u)
and the likelihood
\mathsf{l}(y|u)
for any
pair
(y,u)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}.
We are given data in the form of independent samples
\{y^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}

from the marginal distribution
\kappa
.

Recall that
u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}(y)
minimizes the MAP objective function
{\mathsf{J}}_{\mathsf{MAP}}(\cdot\,;y):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
defined, for each
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
by (
1.9
), (
1.10
), and (
1.11
):

{\mathsf{J}}_{\mathsf{MAP}}(u;y)=-\log\mathsf{l}(y|u)-\log\rho({u}).

In Chapter
1
this method is applied for a given fixed observation
y.

Here our goal is to learn the mapping
y\mapsto u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}(y)
. We approximate this mapping by a function
u^{\star}\in\mathcal{R}_{\Theta}
.
By minimizing the average of the MAP objective function over
y
from its marginal
\kappa
we seek a good approximation, in
\mathcal{R}_{\Theta}
, of the mapping
y\mapsto u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}(y)
over the support of
\kappa
.
To determine the value
\theta^{\star}\in\Theta
which characterizes the function
u^{\star}
we
consider the optimization problem

(5.2a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\kappa}\bigl[\log\mathsf{l}(y|u(y;\theta))+\log\rho({u}(y;\theta))\bigr],

(5.2b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta),

for
u(\cdot\,;\theta)\in\mathcal{R}_{\Theta}.
Then
u^{\star}=u(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star}).

In practice we approximate the expectation defining the optimization problem by
using the empirical measure
\kappa^{N}
defined by
\{y^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
.

Remark 5.3
.

When we derived the objective function to be minimized for MAP estimation in Subsection
1.2.1
, we
dropped the term
\log Z^{y}
which appears in the expression for the negative log posterior. This term remains irrelevant
here, even though we are now computing an expectation over
y
, because it is again independent of
\theta.

∎

5.1.2
Amortized MAP Estimation Using Supervised Learning

In this subsection we consider the same problem as in the preceding subsection,
but under the following variation on Data Assumption
5.2
:

Data Assumption 5.4
.

We are given multiple pairs of observations and numerically computed MAP estimators

\bigl\{(y^{(n)},u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}^{(n)})\bigr\}_{n=1}^{N}
, drawn i.i.d. from a probability measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Remark 5.5
.

The canonical example of measure
\mu
is simply
(\mathsf{id},u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}})_{\sharp}\kappa
so that the data are
simply
\bigl\{\bigl(y^{(n)},u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}(y^{(n)})\bigr)\bigr\}_{n=1}^{N}
with the
y^{(n)}
drawn i.i.d. from probability
measure
\kappa
on
\mathbb{R}^{k}.
We can hence apply the ideas of supervised learning from Chapter
12
to learn the
map
y\mapsto u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}(y)
from class
\mathcal{R}_{\Theta}.

The existence of such paired data will typically have required knowledge of the likelihood and prior,
as assumed explicitly in Data Assumption
5.2
, but the methodology may be applied simply by working
with pairs
(y,u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}})
drawn from any measure
\mu.

∎

Example 5.6
.

As just discussed, when the MAP estimator is a deterministic function of
the data, the joint measure is
\mu=(\mathsf{id},u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}})_{\sharp}\kappa
.
However, since the output of the MAP estimation algorithm will be random if computed with stochastic optimization algorithms such as stochastic gradient descent (Chapter
15
), we work with a general measure
\mu.
Indeed it is possible to generalize away from using the MAP estimator, and simply use the joint measure
\gamma
underlying the inverse problem,
as we will do later in Data Assumption
5.8
.
∎

Given measure
\mu
, we may try and learn the dependence of the MAP estimator on observations as a supervised learning problem; see Chapter
12
and Remark
12.8
. To this end we seek
a parameterized function
u\in\mathcal{R}_{\Theta}
. Recall Definition
11.81
from Subsection
11.3.8

of distance-like deterministic scoring rules
\mathsf{d}
.
We use the scoring rule to compare the point estimator
u(\cdot\,;\theta)\in\mathcal{R}_{\Theta}
, evaluated
at point
y
, to
u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}
.
We then define the optimal value
\theta^{\star}\in\Theta
for
u(\cdot\,;\theta)\in\mathcal{R}_{\Theta}
, by

(5.3)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)&=\mathbb{E}^{(y,u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}})\sim\mu}\,\bigl[\mathsf{d}\bigl(u(y;\theta),u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}\bigr)\bigr],\\
\theta^{\star}&\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta),\end{split}

and set
u^{\star}=u(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star}).
Example
5.1
provides a class
\mathcal{R}_{\Theta}
in which to seek solutions capturing the

\theta
-dependence. In practice we replace the expectation over
\mu
in (
5.3
) with an expectation with respect to
\mu^{N},
the empirical measure defined by
\bigl\{\bigl(y^{(n)},u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}^{(n)}\bigr)\bigr\}_{n=1}^{N}
.

Example 5.7
.

Assume that the algorithm to find the MAP estimator from observed data is defined by a deterministic function
\mathsf{map}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Then
\mu=(\mathsf{id},\mathsf{map})_{\sharp}\kappa
is the pushforward of

\kappa
under the map
y\mapsto\bigl(y,\mathsf{map}(y)\bigr)
.
The
optimization problem becomes

(5.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)&=\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\kappa}\,\bigl[\mathsf{d}\bigl(u(y;\theta),\mathsf{map}(y)\bigr)\bigr],\\
\theta^{\star}&\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).\end{split}

∎

5.1.3
Amortized Posterior Mean Estimation

In this subsection we consider amortizing the posterior mean estimator. We assume that we have samples from the joint distribution on observations and parameters:

Data Assumption 5.8
.

We are given samples
\{(y^{(n)},u^{(n)})\}_{n=1}^{N}
assumed to be drawn i.i.d. from the joint probability measure
\gamma
on
(y,u)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Recall that we denote the marginal of
\gamma
on
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
by
\kappa
.
The following theorem shows that the posterior mean is the minimizer of the squared error, in expectation over the joint distribution
\gamma
.

Theorem 5.9
.

Let
v:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
denote any
\kappa
-measurable function. Then

(5.5)

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(y,u)\sim\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|v(y)-u|^{2}}}\right]\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(y,u)\sim\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(y)-u|^{2}}}\right].

Furthermore, the inequality is strict if
v(y)\neq u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(y)
for
\kappa
-almost every
y
.

Proof.

From Theorem
1.17
, for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
we have that

\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\pi^{y}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|v(y)-u|^{2}}}\right]\geq\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\pi^{y}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(y)-u|^{2}}}\right]

Taking the expectation with respect to
y\sim\kappa
of both sides of this inequality, we obtain the desired inequality. It also follows that the minimizer
u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(y)
is unique.
∎

We now consider finding a parameterized function
u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(\cdot\,;\theta)\in\mathcal{R}_{\Theta}
to approximate the posterior mean. Theorem
5.9
suggests the following optimization problem:

(5.6a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(y,u)\sim\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm PM}}}(y;\theta)-u|^{2}}}\right],

(5.6b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).

In practice, the optimization can be carried out by empirically approximating the expectation using the samples
\{(y^{(n)},u^{(n)})\}_{n=1}^{N}
from Data Assumption
5.8
.

Remark 5.10
.

In fact, (
5.6
) is the standard supervised learning objective to learn a function mapping
y
to
u
, given data from a joint distribution
\gamma
on the pair
(y,u).
Supervised learning is the topic of Chapter
12
; but note that in that chapter we learn functions mapping
u
to
y
and that we work under a more restrictive assumption on the structure of the joint distribution.
∎

5.2
Amortizing Variational Inference

In this section we go beyond amortized point estimators, and generalize variational
inference, introduced in Chapter
2
, to allow for dependence of the approximate posterior
distribution on the data
y
. This leads to the concept of amortized variational
inference.
To this end we introduce parameter set
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
let
\mathcal{Q}_{\Theta}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote
a parameterized set of probability density functions with property that

q(\cdot\,;y,\theta)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
(y,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\Theta.

Thus we aim to learn dependence of the approximate posterior on the observed data
y
. To do this
we need to parameterize not only the probability measure, but its dependence on
y
.

Example 5.11
.

The amortized variational inference problem can be implemented by defining
\mathcal{Q}_{\Theta}
to contain Gaussians,
where the parameters defining the Gaussian are themselves learnable functions of the observation
y
.
This is a generalization of the family of Gaussians in Subsection
2.2.2
, to include
y
-dependence of the mean and covariance.
It leads to a density of the form

(5.7)

q(u;y,\theta)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d}\det\Sigma(y;\theta)}}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{2}\bigl|u-m(y;\theta)\bigr|_{\Sigma(y;\theta)}^{2}}}\right).

The dependence of mean and covariance on
y
will itself need to be parameterized,
for example using neural networks, random features, or Gaussian processes (see Chapter
12
).
Thus parameterizations will need to be constrained to ensure the covariance is a positive definite
matrix for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
; see Example
4.8
for an illustration of how this may be
achieved.
∎

We study variational inference in a general class
\mathcal{Q}_{\Theta}
, noting that this defines a set of probability measures depending on
y
; the parameter
\theta\in\Theta
is selected to learn the best approximation from within this class.
To learn
y
-dependence we require information about the inverse problem for multiple realizations of the data.
In Subsection
5.2.1
we return to work under Data Assumption
5.2
, assuming only access to
observational data
\{y^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
from the marginal
\kappa.

We make Data Assumption
5.12
in Subsection
5.2.2
: we assume that, in addition to the samples from
\kappa
,
we have samples
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
from the prior
\rho.

Finally, in Subsections
5.2.3
and
5.2.4
, we work under Data Assumption
5.8
in a likelihood-free setting, assuming samples

\{\bigl(y^{(n)},u^{(n)}\bigr)\}_{n=1}^{N}
from the joint distribution
\gamma.

In Subsection
5.2.3
we use an objective function defined using
KL divergence and in Subsection
5.2.4
we use strictly proper
scoring rules.

5.2.1
Samples From the Marginal on
y

In this subsection we work again under Data Assumption
5.2
.
With the goal of generalizing variational inference to learn the dependence of the optimal approximation
on the observational data
y
, we define the optimization problem, for
q(\cdot\,;y,\theta)\in\mathcal{Q}_{\Theta},
by

(5.8a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{F}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\kappa}\bigl[\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(q(\cdot\,;y,\theta\bigr)\|\pi^{y})\bigr],

(5.8b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{F}}(\theta).

By averaging the standard divergence used to define variational inference
over
y
drawn from
\kappa
we aim to find an element in
\mathcal{Q}_{\Theta}
that approximates the
y
-dependence of a variational
minimizer, over the support of
\kappa
.
Using the calculations underlying the proof of Theorem
2.1

we see that this optimization problem is equivalent to

(5.9a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\kappa}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(q(\cdot\,;y,\theta)\|\rho\bigr)-\mathbb{E}^{u\sim q(\cdot\,;y,\theta)}\bigl[\log\mathsf{l}(y|u)\bigr]}}\right],

(5.9b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).

Remark
5.3
, suitably modified, also applies here: the
normalization constant for the posterior depends on
y
, however, it is independent of
\theta
and hence not relevant to the optimization.

The objective function
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)
can be evaluated approximately, using samples to approximate the expectation with respect to
y
,
under Data Assumption
5.2
.
We refer to Example
5.11
for an explicit actionable choice of
\mathcal{Q}_{\Theta}.

However, typically some quadrature will be needed, in particular
to evaluate expectations against
q(\cdot\,;y,\theta)
, as discussed in the introduction to
standard variational inference in Section
2.2
.
This is the case for the Gaussian expectations arising from Example
5.11
.

5.2.2
Samples From the Marginals on
y
and on
u

We now make further demands on our available data, extending Data Assumption
5.2

to work under the assumption that we also have prior samples available:

Data Assumption 5.12
.

We are able to evaluate the likelihood
\mathsf{l}(y|u)
and prior
\rho(u)
for any
pair
(y,u)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}.
We are given data in the form of independent samples
\{y^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}

from the marginal distribution
\kappa
and independent samples
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
from the prior distribution
\rho.

Generalizing the transport approach to variational inference described in Section
4.2
, we can define the family of approximating distributions by using a transport map that pushes forward the prior
\rho
to the posterior
\pi^{y}
, and is also parameterized by
y.
In this case, we seek a class
\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}
of transport maps
T\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\Theta\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
that depend on both input parameters and observations so that
u\mapsto T(u;y,\theta)
defines a transport map that is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism
2
2

2

Recall that a diffeomorphism is a
C^{1}
map whose inverse is also
C^{1}
.

with positive Jacobian determinant, which (approximately) pushes forward the prior

\rho
to the posterior
\pi^{y}
, for each choice of the observations
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\theta\in\Theta
:

(5.10)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}\subset\Bigl\{T(\cdot\,;y,\theta)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}),\,\forall(y,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\Theta\,

\displaystyle\Bigl|\det D_{u}T(u;y,\theta)>0,\,\forall(u,y,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\Theta\Bigr\}.

Example 5.13
.

Examples of such classes of maps may be found in Section
13.3
where normalizing flows are introduced.
In particular, neural ODEs,
introduced in Subsection
13.3.2
, provide an example of
\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}.
In the current context they need to be
generalized to include parametric dependence of the defining vector fields on
y
.
We introduce a family of neural networks
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, as discussed in Remark
12.6
and
the material preceding it. We then consider the ODE

(5.11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{dv}{dt}&=h(v;y,\theta),\\
v(0)&=u.\end{split}

Noting that
v(t)
depends on
(u,y,\theta)
, we set

T(u;y,\theta)=v(1).
This defines class
\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}.
The discussion in Subsection
13.3.2

on inversion of the map defined by the neural ODE also applies here.
∎

We seek this transport in
\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}
for
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
as the minimizer of the following objective

(5.12a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{F}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\kappa}\bigl[\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(T(\cdot\,;y,\theta)_{\sharp}\rho\|\pi^{y}\bigr)\bigr]

(5.12b)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\kappa}\bigl[\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\rho\|T^{-1}(\cdot\,;y,\theta)_{\sharp}\pi^{y}\bigr)\bigr],

(5.12c)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{F}}(\theta).

In going from (
5.12
a) to (
5.12
b) we have used the
invariance of the KL divergence under invertible and differentiable transformations from Theorem
11.46
.
By specifying a class of transport maps, we have implicitly defined a class of
y
-dependent probability
measures
\mathcal{Q}_{\Theta}
over which the amortized variational inference
is performed. This is the amortized analog of the discussion in Subsection
4.2.4
.

Following the approach in Chapter
4
, and equation (
4.6
) in particular, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\rho\|T^{-1}(\cdot\,

\displaystyle;y,\theta)_{\sharp}\pi^{y}\bigr)=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}[\log\rho(u)]+\log Z^{y}

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}\Bigl[\log\rho\bigl(T(u;y,\theta)\bigr)+\log\mathsf{l}\bigl(y|T(u;y,\theta)\bigr)+\log{\rm det}D_{u}T(u;y,\theta)\Bigr],

where the first and second terms on the right-hand side of the equality, the negative entropy of
\rho
and
\log Z^{y}
, are constant with respect to
\theta
.
Thus, we can rewrite the optimization problem (
5.12
) as a problem that may be
approximated empirically under Data Assumption
5.12
:

(5.13a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}^{(y,u)\sim\kappa\otimes\rho}\Bigl[\log\rho\bigl(T(u;y,\theta)\bigr)+\log\mathsf{l}\bigl(y|T(u;y,\theta)\bigr)+\log\det D_{u}T(u;y,\theta)\Bigr],

(5.13b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).

Note that this optimization problem is the analog of (
4.6
), arising in the case of a fixed single instance of data
y.

5.2.3
Samples From the Joint Distribution on
(y,u)
; Kullback–Leibler

In this subsection we assume that samples are available from the joint distribution underlying the Bayesian inverse problem defined by equation (
1.1
); that is, we make Data Assumption
5.8
, which does not require
explicit knowledge of the prior or the likelihood.
We have already shown that it is possible to
learn the posterior mean, and its dependence on observation
y
, from this
data set (Subsection
5.1.3
); we now show that it is in fact possible
to learn the posterior distribution from such data.

We do not assume that the likelihood is defined by equation (
1.1
) and Assumption
1.1
. While the methodology in this subsection also applies in that specific setting, it is much more general. Indeed, since the methodology in this subsection depends only on having paired
(y,u)
samples, it is a form of
likelihood-free inference
. This setting often arises when the forward model involves complex simulations; such methods are thus also called
simulation-based inference
. In particular, the methodological approach introduced in this subsection will be used for simulation-based inference problems appearing in the context of
data assimilation
in Subsection
10.4
.

General Formulation

Recall the parameterized subset
\mathcal{Q}_{\Theta}
of
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in which
we seek to solve the variational Bayesian problem. We define the optimization problem, for

q(\cdot\,;y,\theta)\in\mathcal{Q}_{\Theta}
,

(5.14a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{F}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\kappa}\bigl[\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\pi^{y}\|q(\cdot\,;y,\theta)\bigr)\bigr],

(5.14b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{F}}(\theta).

Note that this is identical to optimization problem (
5.8
),
which we employ when making Data Assumption
5.2
, except that we have used the reversed
Kullback–Leibler divergence. This reversal has profound effects
on when this optimization problem can serve as the basis for computational methods.
To see this, note that

\displaystyle{\mathsf{F}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\kappa}\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\pi^{y}}\bigl[\log\bigl(\pi^{y}(u)\bigr)-\log\bigl(q(u;y,\theta)\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{(u,y)\sim\gamma}\bigl[\log\bigl(\pi^{y}(u)\bigr)-\log\bigl(q(u;y,\theta)\bigr)\bigr].

Since the first term in the resulting expression is independent of
\theta
,
we see that optimization problem (
5.14
) is equivalent to

(5.15a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}^{(u,y)\sim\gamma}\bigl[\log\bigl(q(u;y,\theta)\bigr)\bigr],

(5.15b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).

Remark 5.14
.

As mentioned following Data Assumption
5.8
, the methodology in this subsection does not require knowledge of a likelihood.
However it is possible that the samples in Data Assumption
5.8
may have been generated by sampling
u^{(n)}
from the prior
\rho

and then sampling synthetic observations from a likelihood model
y^{(n)}\sim\mathsf{l}(\cdot\;|u^{(n)})
.
Even if the samples are generated from the likelihood model, this does not necessarily imply that the likelihood is available in closed form, as we explain in the next remark.
∎

Remark 5.15
.

One setting in which the likelihood
\mathsf{l}(y|u)
is not analytically available or tractable to evaluate, but is straightforward
to sample, arises when the likelihood is defined through marginalization with respect
to a latent random variable
z.
That is,

(5.16)

\mathsf{l}(y|u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}}\mathbb{P}(y|u,z)\mathbb{P}(z|u)\,dz.

When
d_{z}\gg 1
, it will be difficult to evaluate the integral over
z.
But
we can simulate from the joint distribution
\mathbb{P}(y,u)=\mathsf{l}(y|u)\mathbb{P}(u),
with likelihood
as in (
5.16
), as follows. First sample
u
from the prior
\rho
;
then sample latent state
z
from
\mathbb{P}(z|u)
; and finally sample
y
from

\mathbb{P}(y|u,z)
. The collection
(y,z,u)
is a sample from the distribution
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,z,u)
. The subset of pairs
(y,u)
are then samples from the distribution
\mathsf{l}(y|u)\rho(u)
.

Note that in (
5.16
), the object of interest is obtained by integrating out a (potentially high dimensional) latent variable. This same structure underlies
the expectation maximization methodology,
described in Chapter
15
,
and applied in Chapter
8
. The next example illustrates this structure.

∎

Example 5.16
.

A likelihood of the form in (
5.16
), with high-dimensional latent variable, arises when performing parameter inference in a
hidden Markov model
; noisy observations
y_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
are given of
a stochastic dynamical system for hidden state
v_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}
as:

\displaystyle v_{j+1}

\displaystyle\sim p_{\mathsf{dyn}}(\cdot\,|v_{j};u),

\displaystyle y_{j+1}

\displaystyle\sim p_{\mathsf{obs}}(\cdot\,|v_{j+1}).

Here
v_{0}\sim p_{\mathsf{0}}
is drawn at random from a known probability density
and

p_{\mathsf{dyn}}
is a Markov kernel, parameterized by unknown
u
, defining the evolution of
the hidden variable
V=(v_{0},\dots,v_{J})
. The observations
Y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{J})
are defined by the
known Markov kernel
p_{\mathsf{obs}}.
We are interested in determining
u
from
Y
, and the
likelihood of
Y|u
has the form

(5.17)

\mathsf{l}(Y|u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}(J+1)}}\mathbb{P}(Y|u,V)\mathbb{P}(V|u)\,dV.

This likelihood is typically not available in explicit form, as we now illustrate in a concrete setting.

Hidden Markov models are the common framework for
data assimilation
, which is explored in Chapters
6
–
10
. We follow the formulation in Chapter
6
, but include an unknown parameter
u
, to be determined, to define the dynamics. The states
v_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}
and observations
y_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
follow the dynamics and observation models:

\displaystyle v_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j};u)+\xi_{j},\quad\xi_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma),

\displaystyle y_{j+1}

\displaystyle=h(v_{j+1})+\eta_{j+1},\quad\eta_{j+1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Gamma),

for
j=0,\dots,J-1
starting from initial condition
v_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}
drawn from a known Gaussian

\mathcal{N}(m_{0},C_{0}).

Here,
\Psi\colon\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}
is a forward model depending on the unknown parameter
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Function
h\colon\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}
is a known observation operator.
All random variables in the noise sequences
\{\xi_{j}\}
and
\{\eta_{j}\}
are assumed to be independent.

The conditional probabilities required to define (
5.17
) have the closed forms

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(Y|u,V)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{J}\mathcal{N}\bigl(y_{j};h(v_{j}),\Gamma\bigr),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(V|u)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\prod_{j=0}^{J-1}\mathcal{N}\bigl(v_{j+1};\Psi(v_{j};u),\Sigma\bigr)\Bigr)\mathcal{N}\bigl(v_{0};m_{0},C_{0}\bigr).

The desired likelihood for
Y|u
is given by the integral in (
5.17
).
Comparing to (
5.16
), we see that the hidden states
V
are the latent variables for such a problem. The integration over
V
that is required to render the likelihood in explicit form is not analytically tractable when either
\Psi
or
h
are nonlinear. Hence, such settings require numerical approximations that are computationally expensive for large
J
. This motivates the use of likelihood-free inference methods that do not require (approximate) evaluations of
\mathsf{l}
. A different approach to learn parameters in hidden Markov models, based on expectation maximization, is presented in Chapter
8
.
∎

Transport-Based Formulation

Here we study the solution of optimization problem (
5.15
) through the use of a prior-to-posterior
transport map. As in Subsection
5.2.2
, we generalize the transport approach: we seek parameter
\theta\in\Theta
in map
T\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\Theta\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
so that
u\mapsto T(u;y,\theta)
defines an invertible transport map approximating the pushforward of prior
\rho
to the posterior
\pi^{y}(u)
, for each choice of the observations
y
.
With the selected choice of
\theta
, the resulting map depends on the input state
u

and the observations
y
.

We seek this transport as the minimizer of the following objective, obtained from equation (
5.15
) by taking the distribution
q(\cdot;y,\theta)\in\mathcal{Q}_{\Theta}
to be the pushforward of
\rho
through
T(\cdot;y,\theta)
:

(5.18a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}^{(u,y)\sim\gamma}\bigl[\log\bigl(T(\cdot\,;y,\theta)_{\sharp}\rho(u)\bigr)\bigr]

(5.18b)

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}^{(u,y)\sim\gamma}\Bigl[\log\rho\bigl(T^{-1}(u;y,\theta)\bigr)+\log\det D_{u}(T)^{-1}(u;y,\theta)\Bigr],

(5.18c)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta),

where
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}.
To express the objective, we have used (
11.20
b), which provides an expression for the
density of
T(\cdot\,;y,\theta)_{\sharp}\rho.
If we make
Data Assumption
5.8
this optimization problem can be approximated by sampling, provided that we also have access
to a formula for the prior density
\rho.
The empirical optimization problem takes the form

\theta^{\star}\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}-\Bigl[\log\rho\bigl(T^{-1}(u^{(n)};y^{(n)},\theta)\bigr)+\log\det D_{u}(T)^{-1}(u^{(n)};y^{(n)},\theta)\Bigr].

The optimal solution
\theta^{\star}
computed in practice corresponds to solving the
maximum likelihood
problem for the parameters under the model
T(\cdot\,;\cdot,\theta)_{\sharp}\rho
. See Section
13.1
for more discussion on maximum likelihood estimation.

Remark 5.17
.

When a formula for the prior density is not available, we can seek the transport
T(\cdot;y,\theta)
that approximately pushes forward a general reference measure
\varrho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to the posterior
\pi^{y}
for each
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. That is,
\pi^{y}(u)\approx(T(\cdot;y,\theta)_{\sharp}\varrho)(u)
. The resulting optimization problem for
T
has the form in (
5.18
) with
\rho
replaced by
\varrho
. This setting will be considered in Subsection
10.4
.
∎

Remark 5.18
.

The objective in optimization problem (
5.18
) only depends on the inverse map
T^{-1}(u;y,\theta)
. Hence, rather than parameterizing
T
and evaluating the inverse, we can directly parameterize the inverse map
S(\cdot\,;y,\theta)\coloneqq T^{-1}(\cdot\,;y,\theta)
. This choice avoids inversion during learning; however, it requires inverting the map
S
to sample from the posterior
\pi^{y}
after learning.
∎

The following theorem provides a closed-form solution for optimization problem (
5.18
) when optimizing over a space of affine maps in
u
and
y
.
Recall that
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\textrm{sym},>}
denotes the subset of
symmetric positive definite matrices.

Theorem 5.19
.

Let
\varrho(u)=(2\pi)^{-d/2}\exp(-\frac{1}{2}|u|^{2})
be the standard Gaussian reference density and let the matrices
\Sigma_{uu}
,
\Sigma_{yy}
and
\Sigma_{uy}
denote the covariance of
u
, covariance of
y
and cross-covariance of
(u,y)
under
\gamma
, respectively. Assume that
\Sigma_{yy}
is invertible and that
\Sigma_{u|y}\coloneqq\Sigma_{uu}-\Sigma_{uy}\Sigma_{yy}^{-1}\Sigma_{yu}

is strictly positive definite.

Consider the family of affine maps given by

\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}=\Bigl\{T(u;y,\theta)=Au+By+c,\;A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\textrm{sym},>},B\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k},c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\Bigr\},

parameterized by
\theta:=(A,B,c)
. Then, the optimal map solving (
5.18
) in this family has the form

T(u;y;\theta^{\star})=\Sigma_{u|y}^{1/2}u+\Sigma_{uy}\Sigma_{yy}^{-1}(y-\mathbb{E}[y])+\mathbb{E}[u].

Proof.

Optimization problem (
5.18
) for affine maps
T\in\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}
has the form

\displaystyle(A^{\star},B^{\star},c^{\star})\in\argmin_{A,B,c}\mathbb{E}^{(y,u)\sim\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{2}(u-By-c)^{\top}A^{-\top}A^{-1}(u-By-c)+\log\det A}}\right].

For fixed
A
and
B
, taking the gradient of the objective with respect to
c
and setting it equal to zero, we have

\displaystyle c^{\star}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[u]-B\mathbb{E}[y].

Substituting the optimal
c^{\star}
in the objective, the loss for
B
given a fixed
A
is

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}(B;A)\coloneqq{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{(y,u)\sim\gamma}\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bigl(u-\mathbb{E}[u]-B(y-\mathbb{E}[y])\bigr)^{\top}A^{-\top}A^{-1}\bigl(u-\mathbb{E}[u]-B(y-\mathbb{E}[y])\bigr)}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\log\det A.

Taking the gradient with respect to
B
and setting it equal to zero, we have
D_{B}\mathsf{L}(B;A)=A^{-\top}A^{-1}(B\Sigma_{yy}-\Sigma_{uy})=0.
Rearranging for
B
gives us

B^{\star}=\Sigma_{uy}\Sigma_{yy}^{-1}.

Substituting the optimal
B^{\star}
in the loss
\mathsf{L}
, we notice that the optimization problem for
A^{-\top}A^{-1}
corresponds to the Gaussian variational inference problem in (
2.8
). The optimal solution for
A^{\star}
is given by:

(A^{\star})^{-1}(A^{\star})^{-\top}=\Bigl(\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl(u-\mathbb{E}[u]-\Sigma_{uy}\Sigma_{yy}^{-1}(y-\mathbb{E}[y])\bigr)\bigl(u-\mathbb{E}[u]-\Sigma_{uy}\Sigma_{yy}^{-1}(y-\mathbb{E}[y])\bigr)^{\top}\Bigr]\Bigr)^{-1}.

Expanding the squared terms yields

\displaystyle(A^{\star})^{T}(A^{\star})

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{uu}-\Sigma_{uy}\Sigma_{yy}^{-1}\Sigma_{yu}-\Sigma_{uy}\Sigma_{yy}^{-1}\Sigma_{yu}+\Sigma_{uy}\Sigma_{yy}^{-1}\Sigma_{yy}\Sigma_{yy}^{-1}\Sigma_{yu}

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{uu}-\Sigma_{uy}\Sigma_{yy}^{-1}\Sigma_{yu}

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{u|y},

which gives us the final result,
A^{\star}=\Sigma_{u|y}^{1/2},
in terms of the square root of the conditional covariance matrix, after noting that we
seek
A
as an element of
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\textrm{sym},>}
.
∎

5.2.4
Samples From the Joint Distribution on
(y,u)
; Strictly Proper Scoring Rules

As in the preceding subsection, we again seek an amortized approximation to the posterior distribution in the setting of Data Assumption
5.8
. However,
we now make use of strictly proper scoring rules from Definition
11.49
,
rather than the KL divergence, to define the
objective function. Strictly speaking this takes us outside the standard setting of variational inference,
which is traditionally based on use of the KL divergence.

General Formulation

Consider a joint distribution
\gamma
, with underlying marginal distribution
\kappa
on the observations
y
, and conditional (posterior)
\pi^{y}
on the parameter
u
. The following theorem shows that the posterior is the minimizer of a proper scoring rule, averaged through an expectation over the joint distribution
\gamma
.

Theorem 5.20
.

Consider a strictly proper scoring rule
\mathsf{S}(\cdot,\cdot):\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}.
Let
q(\cdot\,;y)
denote any distribution
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
parameterized by
y
. Then, we have

(5.19)

\mathbb{E}^{(u,y)\sim\gamma}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}(q(\cdot\,;y),u)\Bigr]\geq\mathbb{E}^{(u,y)\sim\gamma}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}(\pi^{y},u)\Bigr].

Furthermore, the inequality is strict if
q(\cdot\,;y)\neq\pi^{y}
.

Proof.

From the definition of a proper scoring rule, we have that

\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\pi^{y}}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}(q(\cdot\,;y),u)\Bigr]\geq\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\pi^{y}}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}(\pi^{y},u)\Bigr].

Taking the expectation with respect to
y\sim\kappa
of both sides of this inequality, we obtain the desired inequality. From strict propriety, we obtain uniqueness of the minimizer.
∎

As in Subsection
5.2.3
, we consider a parameterized subset of
y
-dependent approximate distributions
\mathcal{Q}_{\Theta}
. Then, we can use the cost function in Theorem
5.20
to define an optimization problem over
\theta
, for any given strictly proper scoring rule
\mathsf{S}
:

(5.20a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(u,y)\sim\gamma}[\mathsf{S}(q(\cdot\,;y,\theta),u)],

(5.20b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).

We may then define
q^{\star}(\cdot\,;y):=q(\cdot\,;y,\theta^{\star})\in\mathcal{Q}_{\Theta}
.
In practice, to approximate
\theta^{\star}
, the objective
{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)
is
empiricalized using samples from Data Assumption
5.8
.

Example 5.21
.

If we take the scoring rule
\mathsf{S}
to be the logarithmic score from Definition
11.70
,
\mathsf{LS}(\rho,v)=-\log\rho(v)
, we recover the variational inference optimization problem (
5.15
) from the preceding subsection.
∎

Example 5.22
.

If we take the scoring rule
\mathsf{S}
to be the energy score from
Subsection
11.3.1
, we obtain

(5.21)

{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(u,y)\sim\gamma}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{v\sim q(\cdot\,;y,\theta)}|v-u|-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{y\sim\kappa}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(v,v^{\prime})\sim q(\cdot\,;y,\theta)\otimes q(\cdot\,;y,\theta)}|v-v^{\prime}|.

The objective
{\mathsf{J}}
can then be evaluated by expressing the expectations over distributions that do not depend on the
\theta
parameter. This is desirable
when it is necessary to compute the gradient of the objective function
with respect to
\theta
, for example if using gradient-based
optimization as in Section
15.2
. This is often referred to as the
reparameterization trick
; see Subsection
15.7.3
.
∎

The following example shows how to implement the optimization problem for the Gaussian variational family introduced in
Example
5.11
.

Example 5.23
.

When
\mathcal{Q}_{\Theta}
is the family of multivariate Gaussian distributions
q(\cdot\,;y,\theta)=\mathcal{N}(m(y,\theta),\Sigma(y,\theta))
, we can express a sample from
q
as

m(y,\theta)+\Sigma(y,\theta)^{\frac{1}{2}}\xi\sim q(\cdot\,;y,\theta),\qquad\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),

where
m\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\Theta\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\Sigma\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\Theta\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
are a
y
-dependent mean and covariance matrix;
note that it is desirable that parameterization of the covariance delivers a positive semidefinite matrix so that we can compute its (unique positive) square root. If we again take
\mathsf{S}
to be the energy score from Subsection
11.3.1
, we can write the objective function (
5.21
) as

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(u,y)\sim\gamma}\mathbb{E}^{\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})}|m(y,\theta)+\Sigma(y,\theta)^{\frac{1}{2}}\xi-u|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{y\sim\kappa}\mathbb{E}^{(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})\otimes\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})}|\Sigma(y,\theta)^{\frac{1}{2}}(\xi-\xi^{\prime})|.

Given samples from Data Assumption
5.8
, an empirical approximation of the objective function is given by

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathbb{E}^{\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})}|m(y^{(i)},\theta)+\Sigma(y^{(i)},\theta)^{\frac{1}{2}}\xi-u^{(i)}|}}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}^{(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})\otimes\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})}|\Sigma(y^{(i)},\theta)^{\frac{1}{2}}(\xi-\xi^{\prime})|}}\right].

∎

The preceding example provides a methodology for amortizing inference over a Gaussian family, in which the energy score is used to define
the objective and for which only samples from the joint distribution are
required. The following subsubsection provides a methodology to extend beyond the Gaussian family of distributions.

Transport-Based Formulation

We now study the amortized posterior approximation by minimization of a strictly proper scoring rule through the use of a prior-to-posterior transport map. As in Subsection
5.2.3
, we seek a transport map
T\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\Theta\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
depending on the observation
y
that defines elements of the family
\mathcal{Q}_{\Theta}
as

(5.22)

q(u;y,\theta)=\bigl(T(\cdot\,;y,\theta)_{\sharp}\rho\bigr)(u).

Using the pushforward expression in (
5.20a
),
the objective function can be expressed as

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(u,y)\sim\gamma}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}\bigl(T(\cdot\,;y,\theta)_{\sharp}\rho,u\bigr)\Bigr].

In order to compute this objective under Data Assumption
5.8
, we must use a scoring rule that can be evaluated for an empirical measure. Kernel scores (Definition
11.74
), which include the energy score and the CRPS, are examples. For a choice of kernel
c:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, we can write the optimization problem as

(5.23a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(u,y)\sim\gamma}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{z\sim\rho}[c(T(z;y,\theta),u)]-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{y\sim\kappa}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(z,z^{\prime})\sim\rho\otimes\rho}[c(T(z;y,\theta),T(z^{\prime};y,\theta))],

(5.23b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).

The objective is readily empiricalized, under Data Assumption
5.8
, noting that samples from
\rho
and
\kappa
can be
found by marginalization.

Remark 5.24
.

For a standard Gaussian prior distribution
\rho=\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
, writing the transport as
T(z;y,\theta)=m(y,\theta)+\Sigma(y,\theta)^{\frac{1}{2}}z
in terms of a mean
m
and covariance
\Sigma
, and choosing the kernel to be
c(u,v)=|u-v|
, reduces to the objective in Example
5.23
.
∎

5.3
Learning Likelihood Models

Everything in Subsections
5.2.3
and
5.2.4
is performed
under Data Assumption
5.8
, which is symmetric with respect to the roles of
u
and
y
.
We have used this data to learn the posterior, which is one conditional defined by the joint;
but the same ideas can also be used to learn the other conditional, the likelihood defined by the joint. Learning the likelihood may be useful in inverse problems where the likelihood is unknown analytically or intractable to evaluate, as in Remark
5.15
and Example
5.16
. It may also be natural to learn the prior (as explained in Chapter
3
) and the likelihood (as explained here)
as the basis for sampling the posterior using MCMC; in particular this may be more
effective than learning the posterior directly from the joint, in some circumstances. Another
approach to learning the likelihood, in cases where it is unknown or intractable, is to use surrogate modelling in inference, as discussed in Section
1.6
.

To approximate the likelihood function, we introduce the parameterized subset
\mathcal{Q}_{\Theta}
of
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
, the set of distributions on the observation space, which also depend on an unknown
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The following examples consider two families of distributions
\mathcal{Q}_{\Theta}
. First, Example
5.25
defines a family of Gaussian likelihood functions with a parameter-dependent forward model. Second, Example
5.26
defines a family of conditional distributions on the observation space via transport.
Example
5.27
combines elements of the two preceding examples.

Example 5.25
.

Given a family of forward models,
G\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and a positive definite matrix
\Gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
, a Gaussian conditional distribution for
y|u
with
u
-dependent mean is

\displaystyle q(y;u,\theta)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{k}\det{\Gamma}}}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{2}|y-G(u;\theta)|_{\Gamma}^{2}}}\right).

That is,
q(\cdot\,;u,\theta)=\mathcal{N}(G(u;\theta),\Gamma)
. Thus we are learning, from a parameterized class, the forward model
underlying the Bayesian inverse problem from Section
1.1
. Within this class it might also be of interest to learn the pair

(\theta,\Gamma)
, rather than just
\theta.

∎

Example 5.26
.

Let
\varrho(y)
be the standard Gaussian reference measure on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
and let
T\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}

be a parameter-dependent transport that pushes forward
\varrho(y)
to
q(y;u,\theta)
for each
u
. Then, a conditional distribution for
y|u
is given by

(5.24)

q(y;u,\theta)=\varrho\bigl(T^{-1}(y;u,\theta)\bigr)\det{D_{y}T^{-1}(y;u,\theta)},

where
T^{-1}(\cdot\,;u,\theta)
denotes the inverse of the function
y\mapsto T(y;u,\theta)
. By choosing
T(y;u,\theta)=\Gamma^{1/2}y+G(u;\theta)
, the distribution in (
5.24
) reduces to the Gaussian likelihood in Example
5.25
.
∎

To learn the
\theta
parameters, we follow the approach in Subsection
5.2.3
with the order of the variables
(u,y)
reversed. Our objective is the KL divergence from the
likelihood function
\mathsf{l}(\cdot|u)
to the approximate distribution
q(\cdot\,;u,\theta)
:

(5.25a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{F}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}\bigl[\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\mathsf{l}(\cdot|u)\|q(\cdot\,;u,\theta)\bigr)\bigr],

(5.25b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{F}}(\theta).

As in Subsection
5.2.3
, this optimization problem is equivalent to minimizing an objective that only depends on the joint distribution
\gamma
:

(5.26a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}^{(u,y)\sim\gamma}\bigl[\log\bigl(q(y;u,\theta)\bigr)\bigr],

(5.26b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).

Approximating the expectation using the empirical measure associated with
Data Assumption
5.8
,
we obtain an implementable algorithm to learn the likelihood.
The following example
5.27
combines the two preceding examples to illustrate the general methodology.

Example 5.27
.

For the family of parameterized transport maps in Example
5.26
, the objective defined by the optimization problem (
5.26a
) is equivalent to

(5.27a)

\displaystyle\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}

\displaystyle\,-\mathbb{E}^{(y,u)\sim\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log\varrho\circ T^{-1}(y;u,\theta)+\log\det D_{y}T^{-1}(y;u,\theta)}}\right].

In particular, for the family of Gaussian likelihood models defined by transports of the form
T(y;u,\theta)=\Gamma^{1/2}y+G(u;\theta)

with known
\Gamma\succ 0
and a standard Gaussian distribution
\varrho
, minimizing the loss in (
5.27a
) corresponds to solving the problem

\displaystyle\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}

\displaystyle\,\,\mathbb{E}^{(y,u)\sim\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|T^{-1}(y;u,\theta)}}\right|^{2}-\log\det D_{y}T^{-1}(y;u,\theta)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}\,\,\mathbb{E}^{(y,u)\sim\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\Gamma^{-1/2}\bigl(y-G(u;\theta)\bigr)}}\right|^{2}-\log\det\Gamma^{-1/2}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}\,\,\mathbb{E}^{(y,u)\sim\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|y-G(u;\theta)}}\right|_{\Gamma}^{2}}}\right].

Hence, finding the transport is equivalent to finding an approximate forward model by the solution of a supervised learning problem similar
to that defined in Chapter
12
. Broader families of transports then generalize the approach of learning the forward model in Section
1.6
to learning the likelihood function.
∎

5.4
Learning Regularizers and Priors

The three preceding sections have considered a range of data assumptions, all centered on multiple observational
data
\{y^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
from the inverse problem defined by (
1.1
):

y=G(u)+\eta.

We have used this data to learn about the posterior distribution or likelihood.
In this section we use such data
to learn about regularizers or priors for the inverse problem.
This is different from Chapter
3
where we discussed
learning the prior, or regularizer for MAP estimation; in that
chapter the training data from which we learn or define the prior was
unsupervised data
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
or, in the case of Bayesian
hierarchical modelling, simply the single observation
y
. Here the use of
multiple observational
data realizations
\{y^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
can be thought of as limiting over-fitting
to one specific observed outcome.
In Subsection
5.4.1
we consider regularizers for MAP estimation
and in Subsection
5.4.2
we study priors for Bayesian inversion.

5.4.1
Learning Regularizers for MAP Estimation

In this section we describe the idea of
bilevel optimization
to determine
parameter
\theta\in\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
defining a regularizer in MAP estimation.
We return to work under a modification of Data Assumption
5.8
to include evaluation of likelihood
and a parameterized prior – Data Assumption
5.28
.

Paired
(y,u)
data, as in Data Assumption
5.8
, is exactly what is required for
the ideas of supervised learning from Chapter
12
, where
we use it to learn map between
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathbb{R}
, and this is readily
generalized to vector-valued output in
\mathbb{R}^{q}
– see Remark
12.6
.
Note that, to solve the inverse problem, we would like
to learn the inverse map from the data
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
to
the parameter
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
We could try and do this directly using supervised
learning. However, for inverse problems in which
k<d,

learning such a map may be difficult because of a lack of uniqueness; furthermore there is
noise present in the
y^{(n)}
which needs to be carefully accounted for.
For these reasons it is arguably more informative to learn, from
the supervised data defined by Data Assumption
5.8
, how to regularize the inverse problem.
This is the viewpoint we take here.

We recall the posterior probability density function
\pi^{y}(u)
on
u|y
from Theorem
1.3
, but assume that prior
\rho

depends on an unknown parameter
\theta\in\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
so that the posterior has form

(5.28)

\pi^{y}({u};\theta)=\frac{1}{Z^{y}(\theta)}\nu\bigl({y}-{G}({u})\bigr)\rho({u};\theta).

We will determine
\theta
by choosing it
so that MAP estimators for the inverse problem defined by data
y^{(n)}
best match the paired data point
u^{(n)}
.
To this end recall the
loss function

\mathsf{L}

and the now
\theta
-dependent
regularizer

\mathsf{R}
defined by

(5.29)

\mathsf{L}({u};y)=-\log\nu\bigl({y}-{G}({u})\bigr)=-\log\mathsf{l}(y|u),\quad\mathsf{R}({u};\theta)=-\log\rho({u};\theta),

leading to
an
objective function
of the form

(5.30)

{\mathsf{J}}({u};y,\theta)=\mathsf{L}({u};y)+\mathsf{R}({u};\theta).

Note that we have also emphasized the parametric dependence of the loss and the objective function on
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
something we did not do in Section
1.2
.
The generalization of Data Assumption
5.8
that we now work with is

Data Assumption 5.28
.

We are able to evaluate the likelihood
\mathsf{l}(y|u)
and prior
\rho(u;\theta)
for any
pair
(y,u,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta.

We are given samples
\{(y^{(n)},u^{(n)})\}_{n=1}^{N}
, assumed to be drawn i.i.d. from
the joint probability measure
\gamma
on
(y,u)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
defined by equation (
1.1
) and Assumption
1.1
.

The MAP estimator can be viewed as a function, for each
\theta,

u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}(\cdot\,;\theta):\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

\displaystyle u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}(y;\theta)

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathsf{J}}(u;y,\theta).

(For simplicity we assume that a unique point is determined by the minimization.)
We now introduce a loss function defined through a distance-like deterministic
scoring rule
\mathsf{d}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+},
as defined in Definition
11.81
; the canonical example is the squared Euclidean norm. We then define the choice of
\theta
through the optimization problem

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{E}^{(y,u)\sim\gamma}\,\mathsf{d}\bigl(u,u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}(y;\theta)\bigr).

This is referred to as bilevel optimization because of the optimization to
find
u_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}
, which is used within the optimization
to find
\theta^{\star}
. This procedure corresponds to hyperparameter tuning of
\theta
on the validation set
\{(y^{(n)},u^{(n)})\}_{n=1}^{N}
. In practice, the determination of
\theta^{\star}
is implemented using
\gamma^{N}
, the approximation of
\gamma
found from empirical samples
\{(y^{(n)},u^{(n)})\}_{n=1}^{N}
drawn i.i.d. from
\gamma
, as assumed in Data Assumption
5.28
.

5.4.2
Learning Priors for Posterior Approximation

In this subsection we generalize the setting from the previous one to the more general problem of learning parameters in the prior to best approximate the posterior. We work under the following data assumption, which differs from that in the previous subsection:
we assume only marginal samples from
\kappa
and
\rho
,
but this is compensated for by employing evaluation of the likelihood.
We return to work under the following modification of Data Assumption
5.12
:

Data Assumption 5.29
.

We are able to evaluate the likelihood
\mathsf{l}(y|u)
and prior
\rho(u;\theta)
for any
pair
(y,u,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta.

We are given data in the form of independent samples
\{y^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}

from the marginal distribution
\kappa
and independent samples
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
from the prior distribution
\rho.

Note the difference between Data Assumption
5.12
and Data Assumption
5.29
:
the point of this subsection is to learn the prior density, and we extend the assumption to allow
for evaluation of a parametrically dependent family of priors.

Remark 5.30
.

In Data Assumption
5.12
and in Data Assumption
5.29
the set of independent samples
\{y^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}

and the set of independent samples
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
do not need to be independent of one another,
and the same number of each is not required. In practice they may be found by marginalizing a data set of joint samples
\{(y^{(n)},u^{(n)})\}_{n=1}^{N}
from
\gamma
. It is then natural to have the same number of samples of each, and for each set of samples to be correlated. Thus, in this subsection, we may also work under Data Assumption
5.8
provided that, additionally, we can evaluate the likelihood
\mathsf{l}(y|u)
and prior
\rho(u;\theta)
for any pair
(y,u,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta.

∎

It is again helpful to recall the notation from Section
1.1
and Remark
1.5
. The goal is to choose parameters
\theta
in the prior so that the resulting posterior
\pi^{y}(u;\theta)
in (
5.28
) is as close as possible to the true posterior
\pi^{y}(u)
corresponding to the joint distribution
\gamma(y,u)=\mathsf{l}(y|u)\rho(u)
underlying the data given in Data Assumption
5.29
. We identify the parameters by minimizing the posterior error with respect to the KL divergence,
in expectation over the marginal on
y
which, we recall, has probability distribution
\kappa
:

(5.31a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{F}}(\theta)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\kappa}\Bigl[\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\pi^{y}\|\pi^{y}(\cdot\,;\theta)\bigr)\Bigr],

(5.31b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{F}}(\theta).

The following result shows that the optimal parameters can be computed without needing to evaluate the true posterior density.

Theorem 5.31
.

The optimal parameter
\theta^{\star}
in (
5.31b
) corresponds to the solution of the optimization problem

(5.32a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle:=\biggl(\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log\rho(u;\theta)}}\right]+\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\kappa}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log\int\mathsf{l}(y|u^{\prime})\rho(u^{\prime};\theta)\,du^{\prime}}}\right]\biggr),

(5.32b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).

Proof.

We first note that
\gamma(y,u)=\pi^{y}(u)\kappa(y).
Thus

(5.33a)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\kappa}\Bigl[\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\pi^{y}\|\pi^{y}(\cdot\,;\theta)\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\pi^{y}(u)}{\pi^{y}(u;\theta)}}}\right)\pi^{y}(u)du}}\right]\kappa(y)\,dy

(5.33b)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\pi^{y}(u)}{\pi^{y}(u;\theta)}}}\right)\gamma(y,u)\,dy\,du.

By (
5.33
), the expected KL divergence in (
5.31a
) can be decomposed into two terms as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{y}\Bigl[\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\pi^{y}\|\pi^{y}(\cdot\,;\theta)\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\int\gamma(y,u)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathsf{l}(y|u)\rho(u)}{Z^{y}}}}\right)-\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathsf{l}(y|u)\rho(u;\theta)}{Z^{y}(\theta)}}}\right)}}\right]\,du\,dy

(5.34)

\displaystyle=\int\gamma(y,u)\Bigl[\bigl(\log\rho(u)-\log Z^{y}\bigr)-\bigl(\log\rho(u;\theta)-\log Z^{y}(\theta)\bigr)\Bigr]\,du\,dy.

The first two terms are constant with respect to
\theta
and are thus irrelevant for the minimization task that is the object of this theorem. Thus we need only minimize

(5.35)

\theta^{\star}\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}\int\gamma(y,u)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log\rho(u;\theta)+\log Z^{y}(\theta)}}\right]\,du\,dy.

Using the form of the normalizing constant
Z^{y}(\theta)=\int\mathsf{l}(y|u^{\prime})\rho(u^{\prime};\theta)\,du^{\prime}
, we arrive at the objective
{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)
, after noticing that in the first term integration over
y
is redundant, and in the
second integration over
u
is redundant.
∎

Note that the objective function in Theorem
5.31
can be approximated
empirically using Data Assumption
5.29
. In particular, this requires
only marginal samples from
\gamma
, and does not require knowledge of the
posterior – evaluation of the likelihood is sufficient.

Remark 5.32
.

In the preceding chapters we have differentiated between methods which require evaluation of
the normalization constant and those which do not. This is because,
for problems for which the likelihood and prior do not have significant overlap, evaluation of the
normalization constant can be expensive. The method in this section does require evaluation of the normalization constant. This is a potentially severe limitation on the methodology in this subsection,
and typically limits it to problems where the parameter
u
is of low dimension.
∎

Remark 5.33
.

The expected KL divergence in (
5.34
) can also be written as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{y}\Bigl[\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\pi^{y}\|\pi^{y}(\cdot\,;\theta)\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\int\rho(u)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log\rho(u)-\log\rho(u;\theta)}}\right]\,du+\int\kappa(y)\log\Biggl(\frac{Z^{y}(\theta)}{Z^{y}}\Biggr)\,dy

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\rho\|\rho(\cdot\,;\theta)\bigr)-\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\kappa\|\kappa(\cdot\,;\theta)\bigr),

where in the last line we recognize that the normalizing constants
Z^{y},Z^{y}(\theta)
are equivalent to the marginal distributions of the observations
\kappa(y)=\int\mathsf{l}(y|u^{\prime})\rho(u^{\prime})\,du^{\prime}
and
\kappa(y;\theta)=\int\mathsf{l}(y|u^{\prime})\rho(u^{\prime};\theta)\,du^{\prime}
, respectively.
Thus, the objective in (
5.31a
) involves two competing terms, the first relating only to the prior, the marginal distribution on the states, and the second to the marginal distribution on the observations.
Note that when this objective is to be
approximated by means of empirical samples from
\rho
and
\kappa
, undefined terms arise in both divergences; these terms are
\theta
-independent and can be removed as we did to arrive at (
5.35
). See Remark
13.4
for discussion of this point in the canonical context of maximum likelihood density estimation.
∎

5.5
Bibliography

Amortized variational inference was introduced in a number of specific contexts within the field
of variational inference. Variational autoencoders, the subject of Section
13.6
and which were proposed concurrently in

[
282
]
and
[
410
]
, implicitly introduced
the concept of amortized inference. In particular the encoder may be thought of
as defining a family of posterior distributions on the latent variable, conditioned
on the different samples in the original space; see Remark
13.20
. This
family may be approximated, for example, as a parameterized family of Gaussians,
as in Example
5.11
. Amortized inference is overviewed in
[
330
,
518
]
and is now
widely used in the context of latent variable models
[
140
,
69
]
.
The idea of iterating amortized inference was introduced in

[
331
]
; the iterative approach is designed to close
the amortization gap caused by failing to reach
optimality when training. Amortized variational inference is
applied to filtering problems in
[
332
]
.
The methodology has also been used to avoid expensive per-instance computation across a range of tasks in meta-learning
[
175
]
and to learn parameters for optimization algorithms
[
25
]
. Amortized MAP estimation of the type discussed in Subsection
5.1.1
is used in
[
463
]
for image super-resolution.

The concept of amortization to perform likelihood-free or simulation-based inference has origins in statistics with high-dimensional latent variables. The latent variable
z
over which we marginalize is often referred to as a
nuisance

or auxiliary random variable; this is often used in the setting where
z
is not the
primary parameter of interest in the inverse problem. An alternative approach to those
described here seeks the joint posterior of
(u,z)
, and then marginalizes after the
computation. While the corresponding likelihood for the joint posterior doesn’t involve a marginal or integrated likelihood, it can lead to a challenging problem for high or even infinite-dimensional latent variables. We refer to
[
51
]
for more details on integrated likelihoods. In MCMC algorithms, these integrated likelihoods are commonly addressed using pseudo-marginal methods that work with unbiased estimators of the likelihood
[
24
]
.
Amortized posterior estimation using the energy score is explored in
[
49
]
.

Approximate Bayesian computation (ABC) is a classic inference method for performing likelihood-free inference with latent variables or other intractable likelihoods; see
[
440
]
for a comprehensive overview on ABC. These approaches define a distance function that compares simulated observations to the true observation and reject parameter samples that are not consistent with the true observation based on a small tolerance. ABC methods can be shown to be consistent in the limit of the tolerance approaching zero, but typically require large sample sizes with high-dimensional observations.

Learning approaches have appeared as alternatives to ABC; machine learning-based approaches for simulation-based inference are outlined in
[
132
]
. Methodologies for both posterior and likelihood approximation in this setting are implemented using various unsupervised learning architectures (see Chapter
13
) including: conditional normalizing flows
[
510
,
373
]
, conditional generative adversarial networks
[
406
]
, conditional diffusion models
[
52
]
.
Triangular transport maps are a core element of these architectures for solving inverse problems. They are related to the Knothe–Rosenblatt transport, which has been used for conditional density estimation in
[
334
,
50
]
. The design and analysis of block-triangular transport maps to solve likelihood-free inference problems on function space is investigated in
[
47
,
251
]
.

Part II
Data Assimilation

Chapter 6
Filtering and Smoothing Problems

This chapter provides an introduction to
data assimilation
.
We study both the
filtering
and the
smoothing
problems. Consider the
stochastic dynamics model

given by

(6.1a)

\displaystyle v^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v^{\dagger}_{j})+\xi^{\dagger}_{j},\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},

(6.1b)

\displaystyle v^{\dagger}_{0}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}(m_{0},C_{0}),\quad\xi^{\dagger}_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)\>\,\,\text{i.i.d.}\,,

combined with the
data model
given by

(6.2a)

\displaystyle y^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=h(v^{\dagger}_{j+1})+\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1},\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},

(6.2b)

\displaystyle\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Gamma)\>\,\,\text{i.i.d.}

Broadly speaking, data assimilation seeks to
find the
state
, or
signal
,
\{v^{\dagger}_{j}\}
over index set
j\in\{0,1,\dots,J\}

based on realized
data
, or
observations

\{y^{\dagger}_{j}\}

over index set
j\in\{1,\dots,J\}.

Various different inverse problems, with
this aim, can be defined; we will introduce them in this chapter.
Indeed, the goal of this chapter is to define the key problems arising in the field of data assimilation,
and to outline the primary methodologies used, prior to the current influx of new ideas from machine learning. These new ideas are then described in the four subsequent chapters.

Unless otherwise noted, we will work throughout this chapter, and the four subsequent chapters
devoted to data assimilation, under the following two assumptions on the stochastic dynamics and data models.
Recall, from the preface, that
\perp\!\!\!\!\perp
denotes independence between random variables. The first assumption
concerns the independence structure of
the random variables entering the signal–observation model:

Assumption 6.1
.

\{\xi^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp\{\eta^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp v^{\dagger}_{0}.

The second assumption concerns the matrices and maps defining the signal–observation model:

Assumption 6.2
.

The matrices
C_{0},

\Sigma
and
\Gamma
are positive definite.
Furthermore, the nonlinear maps
\Psi
and
h
are continuously differentiable:
\Psi\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
h\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
.

Section
6.1
contains introductory remarks that will orient the reader in the remainder
of the chapter.
In Section
6.2
we formulate the filtering and smoothing
problems in detail. In Sections
6.3
and
6.4
, respectively, we describe algorithms for the filtering and smoothing approaches to data assimilation. In Section
6.5
we discuss model error in the context of filtering and smoothing problems. Section
6.6
is
devoted to the study of surrogate modeling, for both filtering and
smoothing; this serves as an introduction to the potential for the use of ideas from
machine learning within data assimilation. In Section
6.7
we describe various generalizations of the setting
in this chapter and, in particular, relaxations of the Assumptions
6.1
and
6.2
. Section
6.8
contains bibliographic notes.

6.1
Introduction

Subsection
6.1.1
is devoted to notation and, in particular, explains
the use of the
\dagger
on the signal–observation models (
6.1
), (
6.2
); Subsection
6.1.2
contrasts the filtering and smoothing approaches to data assimilation; and Subsection
6.1.3
contrasts the probabilistic and state estimation approaches to data assimilation.

6.1.1
Notation

To state the inverse problems of interest precisely, we make the following
definitions: for a given and fixed integer
J,
and for
1\leq j\leq J,

(6.3a)

\displaystyle V^{\dagger}:=

\displaystyle\{v^{\dagger}_{0},\ldots,v^{\dagger}_{J}\},\>\,V^{\dagger}_{j}:=\{v^{\dagger}_{0},\ldots,v^{\dagger}_{j}\},

(6.3b)

\displaystyle\>\,Y^{\dagger}:=

\displaystyle\{y^{\dagger}_{1},\ldots,y^{\dagger}_{J}\},\>\,Y^{\dagger}_{j}:=\{y^{\dagger}_{1},\ldots,y^{\dagger}_{j}\}.

The sequence
V^{\dagger}
is often termed the
signal
or
state

and the sequence
Y^{\dagger}
the
data
or
observation
. It is sometimes helpful
to define
Y^{\dagger}_{0}
as the empty set
\varnothing.

We will occasionally consider the infinite time horizon limit where
J=\infty.

We use the notation
\dagger
throughout Part II of the notes, devoted to data assimilation,
to denote all random variables
associated with the underlying model for signal and data. We adopt this
convention because many of the algorithms that we develop for data assimilation will use the
signal and/or data models (
6.1
), (
6.2
) as part of their backbone. We will
use symbols
v_{j}
, or for ensemble algorithms
v_{j}^{(n)}
with
n
indexing particles in the ensemble, for the
outputs of algorithms to find the state

\{v^{\dagger}_{j}\}
;
these algorithms will operate over some set of time indices
j\in\{0,1,\dots,J\}
,
based on realized observations
Y^{\dagger}
. We will also use the
notation
(\widehat{v}_{j+1},\widehat{y}_{j+1})
, or for ensemble algorithms
(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)}),
for
predictions made as part of algorithms; in particular for predictions of the state
v^{\dagger}_{j+1}
, and predictions of the observation
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
, at time
j+1
using only
observed data
Y^{\dagger}_{j}
up to time
j.

The state predictions
\widehat{v}_{j+1}
or
\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}
,
possibly together with
\widehat{y}_{j+1}
or
\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)}
,
are then corrected using the observed data
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
to yield the output

v_{j+1}
or
v_{j+1}^{(n)}
of algorithms at time
j+1.

By adopting this notational convention we distinguish
between the output of the underlying signal and/or data models (
6.1
), (
6.2
)
(
v
and
y
symbols with
\dagger
) and algorithms to estimate the signal
(
v
and
y
symbols without
\dagger
); this serves to avoid
confusion between the source of data, and the algorithms for data assimilation, when the
latter also use the underlying signal and/or data models as part of their definition.
We do not use the
\dagger
convention in Part I of the notes, devoted to inverse problems, because the algorithms we study do not have the same potential for confusion with output of
the underlying inverse problem model (
1.1
).

6.1.2
Filtering Versus Smoothing

There are two core problems in data assimilation,
one known as
filtering
,
and the other as
smoothing
. Both use:

Data Assumption 6.3
.

Data
Y^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{kJ}
is given and is assumed to have come from
the signal-observation model defined by
(
6.1
) and (
6.2
).

The
filtering distribution
at time
j
is
\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})
: the distribution of the state
v^{\dagger}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
at time
j
, conditioned on data
Y^{\dagger}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{kj}
. This distribution at time
j
is defined without knowledge of future observations at times
j^{\prime}>j.
We say that this distribution is
time-causal.
The objective of filtering is to find
\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})
for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,J\},
sequentially.

The
smoothing distribution
is
\mathbb{P}(V^{\dagger}|Y^{\dagger})
, a probability density function on an entire sequence of states
V^{\dagger}
given the entire dataset
Y^{\dagger}
; note that
V^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{d(J+1)}
and
Y^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{kJ}
represent sequences over a window of length
J+1
and
J
, respectively. For
0\leq j\leq J-1,
the marginal distribution of the smoothing distribution
\mathbb{P}(V^{\dagger}|Y^{\dagger})
, corresponding to time
j,
is
\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger});
this depends
on observations at times
j^{\prime}>j
, highlighting an essential difference between filtering and smoothing.

The smoothing problem is an example of a single Bayesian inverse problem
of the type defined in Chapter
1
. The filtering problem comprises
J
Bayesian inverse problems, interleaved with predictions of the model.
We now describe the Bayesian perspective on filtering and on smoothing.
In smoothing the prior on
V^{\dagger}
is a probability density function defined by
(
6.1
); the posterior is found by conditioning this prior on data
Y^{\dagger}
defined by (
6.2
). In filtering, at time
j+1
, the prior is given by
\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})
which itself is defined by combining
\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})
with the dynamics model (
6.1
); thus we see that the prior for the filtering distribution
at time
j+1
is defined by combining the filtering distribution at time
j

with the dynamics model (
6.1
). The posterior
\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j+1})
is then found by conditioning the prior
\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})
on observation
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
using the data model (
6.2
).

Figure 6.1:
Dynamics and observation models underlying data assimilation problems.

6.1.3
Probabilistic Versus State Estimation

When our goal is to approximate or characterize the filtering and smoothing distributions arising in these Bayesian formulations of the filtering and smoothing problems,
we will refer to
probabilistic estimation.

The underlying inverse problems are solved in a Bayesian fashion.
On the other hand many optimization-based filtering and smoothing algorithms
aim only to provide a point estimate for the state, given the data.
We refer to this as
state estimation.
State estimation
may be thought of as being analogous to MAP estimation, from Subsection
1.2.1
,
in the general Bayesian inverse problem setting.
Indeed, for the smoothing problem, the MAP estimator associated with the posterior
distribution on
V^{\dagger}
is frequently employed as a point estimator of the state. The situation is
