more complicated for filtering; this is because the sequential nature of the problem means that we only have access to approximate priors, often in the form of point masses, or a Gaussian, for example; for this reason iterative computation of the MAP estimator is not always natural. Nonetheless, useful algorithms for state estimation do exist in filtering and they will play an important role in what follows. In many cases they are closely related to classical algorithms from control theory.

6.2
Formulation of Data Assimilation

In Subsection
6.2.1
we formulate the filtering problem
and in Subsection
6.2.2
the
smoothing problem.

6.2.1
Formulation of the Filtering Problem

Definition 6.4
.

The
filtering problem
is to find, and update sequentially in
j
, the probability densities

\pi_{j}(v^{\dagger}_{j}):=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
j=1,\dots,J.

We refer to
\pi_{j}
as the
filtering distribution at time
j.

∎

Filtering may be understood as the sequential interleaving of prediction, using the
stochastic dynamics model (
6.1
), with inversion,
using the data model (
6.2
).
To explain this perspective it is helpful to introduce
1
1

1

Notice that
\widehat{\pi}_{1}:=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{1})
with the convention that
Y^{\dagger}_{0}=\varnothing.

\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})
.
We also define the Gaussian likelihood
\mathsf{l}(y|u)=\mathcal{N}(y;h(u),\Gamma)
,
dictated by the data model (
6.2
).
Combining
\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}
as prior with this likelihood, evaluated at time
j+1
, Bayes theorem delivers the formula

\pi_{j+1}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1})\propto\mathsf{l}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v^{\dagger}_{j+1})\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}).

It is useful to decompose the sequential updating

\pi_{j}\mapsto\pi_{j+1}
into the following two steps, expressed abstractly using maps on
probability measures:

(6.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\text{ \bf Prediction Step:}}~~~~\;\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}={\mathsf{P}}\pi_{j}.\\
&{\text{ \bf Analysis Step:}}~~~~~\,\,\,\,\,\pi_{j+1}={\mathsf{A}}_{j}(\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}):={\mathsf{A}}(\widehat{\pi}_{j+1};y^{\dagger}_{j+1}).\end{split}

The recursion starts at
\pi_{0}
, assumed to be the Gaussian
\mathcal{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
from (
6.1
b).
The combination of the prediction and analysis steps is shown schematically in Figure
6.2
and leads to the update

(6.5)

\pi_{j+1}={\mathsf{A}}_{j}({\mathsf{P}}\pi_{j}).

Here
{\mathsf{P}}
is a linear map,
2
2

2

Strictly speaking
{\mathsf{P}}
is linear when viewed as acting on the vector space
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
; the space of probability density functions is a subset of this vector space, but is not a linear subspace.
defining the Markov process underlying the
stochastic dynamics model. Map
{\mathsf{A}}_{j}={\mathsf{A}}(\cdot\,;y^{\dagger}_{j+1})
is a
nonlinear map defined by application of Bayes Theorem
1.3
to solve the inverse problem
specified by the likelihood
\mathsf{l}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v^{\dagger}_{j+1})
, with prior
\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}
– see Remark
1.4
.

Figure 6.2:
Prediction and analysis steps combined.

The linear prediction operator
{\mathsf{P}}
is defined as follows:

(6.6a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{P}}\pi(u)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(u|v)\pi(v)\,dv

(6.6b)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d}\det\Sigma}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{2}\bigl|u-\Psi(v)\bigr|_{\Sigma}^{2}}}\right)\pi(v)\,dv.

The expression for the Gaussian conditional probability density function
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(u|v)=\mathcal{N}(u;\Psi(v),\Sigma)
is dictated by the given stochastic dynamics model (
6.1
). If the state is distributed according to
\pi
at time
j,
then
{\mathsf{P}}\pi
represents the distribution of the state at time
j+1
when the state evolution is defined by the Markovian dynamics (
6.1
).

To define the analysis operator, notice that any probability density function
\pi
in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be extended to a joint probability density
\gamma_{\pi}
in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k})
as follows:

(6.7a)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\pi}(u,y)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{l}(y|u)\pi(u)

(6.7b)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{k}\det\Gamma}}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{2}\bigl\lvert y-h(u)\bigr\rvert_{\Gamma}^{2}}}\right)\pi(u).

Here, recall,
\mathsf{l}(y|u)
is dictated by the data model (
6.2
). Map
\pi\to\gamma_{\pi}

is linear. However, using it we may define the nonlinear operator
{\mathsf{A}}(\cdot;y)
by

(6.8)

{\mathsf{A}}(\pi;y)(u)=\frac{\gamma_{\pi}(u,y)}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\gamma_{\pi}(u,y)\,du}.

Given a prior
\pi
on the state at time
j+1,

{\mathsf{A}}_{j}(\pi)={\mathsf{A}}(\pi;y^{\dagger}_{j+1})
represents the posterior distribution of the state at time
j+1
determined by data model (
6.2
) and observed data
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}.

6.2.2
Formulation of the Smoothing Problem

Recall the definitions of
V^{\dagger}
and
Y^{\dagger}
from (
6.3
).

Definition 6.5
.

The
smoothing problem
is to find the probability density

\Pi^{Y^{\dagger}}(V^{\dagger}):=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(V^{\dagger}|Y^{\dagger})
on

\mathbb{R}^{d(J+1)}

for some fixed integer
J.
We refer to
\Pi^{Y^{\dagger}}
as the
smoothing distribution
.
∎

Using Bayes Theorem
1.3
, the density for the smoothing distribution
\mathbb{P}(V^{\dagger}|Y^{\dagger})
is given by

(6.9)

\Pi^{Y^{\dagger}}\!(V^{\dagger})\propto\mathsf{l}(Y^{\dagger}|V^{\dagger})\rho(V^{\dagger}).

The signal–observation model defined by (
6.1
) and (
6.2
), together with the standing Assumptions
6.1
and
6.2
, determine the likelihood
\mathsf{l}(Y^{\dagger}|V^{\dagger})
and the prior

\rho(V^{\dagger})
entering this expression. We obtain

(6.10a)

\displaystyle\mathsf{l}(Y^{\dagger}|V^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{J}\mathsf{l}_{j}(v^{\dagger}_{j}),\quad\rho(V^{\dagger})=\Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{J}\mathsf{t}(v^{\dagger}_{j},v^{\dagger}_{j-1})\Bigr)\rho_{0}(v^{\dagger}_{0}),

(6.10b)

\displaystyle\mathsf{l}_{j}(v^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=Z_{\Gamma}^{-1}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2}|y^{\dagger}_{j}-h(v^{\dagger})|^{2}_{\Gamma}\Bigr),\quad Z_{\Gamma}=(2\pi)^{k/2}\det(\Gamma)^{1/2},

(6.10c)

\displaystyle\mathsf{t}(v^{\dagger},w^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=Z_{\Sigma}^{-1}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2}|v^{\dagger}-\Psi(w^{\dagger})|^{2}_{\Sigma}\Bigr),\quad Z_{\Sigma}=(2\pi)^{d/2}\det(\Sigma)^{1/2},

(6.10d)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}(v^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=Z_{C_{0}}^{-1}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2}|v^{\dagger}-m_{0}|_{C_{0}}^{2}\Bigr),\quad Z_{C_{0}}=(2\pi)^{d/2}\det(C_{0})^{1/2}.

Note that the prior on
V^{\dagger}
is given by the dynamics model (
6.1
) and is not Gaussian (unless
\Psi(\cdot)
is linear). The likelihood, defined by (
6.2
), is Gaussian.
For both the likelihood and the prior we have used the independence assumptions encapsulated in
Assumption
6.1
, and the Markovian structure arising from the i.i.d. nature of the sequences

\{\xi^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
and
\{\eta^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
,
to derive the formulae for likelihood and prior as given.
It is also insightful to write the solution of this Bayesian inverse problem in a notation that
removes the indices, to clarify the high-level structure.
To this end we define
\mathsf{\Psi}:\mathbb{R}^{dJ}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d(J+1)}
by

\displaystyle\mathsf{\Psi}(V^{\dagger}_{J-1};m_{0}):=\bigl\{m_{0},\Psi(v^{\dagger}_{0}),\ldots,\Psi(v^{\dagger}_{J-1})\bigr\},

and
\mathsf{\Sigma}
to be a block diagonal matrix with
C_{0}
in the first diagonal block and with
\Sigma
in
all remaining diagonal blocks. From (
6.1
) we have

(6.11)

\rho(V^{\dagger})\propto\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2}|V^{\dagger}-\mathsf{\Psi}\bigl(V^{\dagger}_{J-1};m_{0}\bigr)|_{\mathsf{\Sigma}}^{2}\Bigr).

We define
\eta^{\dagger}:=\{\eta^{\dagger}_{1},\ldots,\eta^{\dagger}_{J}\}\in\mathbb{R}^{kJ}

and then
\eta^{\dagger}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathsf{\Gamma})
, where
\mathsf{\Gamma}
is block diagonal with
\Gamma
in
each diagonal block. If we then define
\mathsf{h}:\mathbb{R}^{d(J+1)}\to\mathbb{R}^{kJ}
by

\mathsf{h}(V^{\dagger}):=\bigl\{h(v^{\dagger}_{1}),\ldots,h(v^{\dagger}_{J})\bigr\},

then the data model (
6.2
) may be written as

Y^{\dagger}=\mathsf{h}(V^{\dagger})+\eta^{\dagger}.

The likelihood thus has the form

(6.12)

\mathsf{l}(Y^{\dagger}|V^{\dagger})\propto\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2}|Y^{\dagger}-\mathsf{h}(V^{\dagger})|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2}\Bigr).

We are interested in finding
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(V^{\dagger}|Y^{\dagger})=\Pi^{Y^{\dagger}}\!(V^{\dagger}).
We may apply Bayes Theorem
1.3

with prior
\rho
and likelihood
\mathsf{l}
, noting that, under Assumption
6.1
,
V^{\dagger}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp\eta^{\dagger},
the standard setting for Bayesian inversion from Chapter
1
. We may then write (
6.9
) in the form

(6.13a)

\displaystyle\Pi^{Y^{\dagger}}\!(V^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\propto\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2}|Y^{\dagger}-\mathsf{h}(V^{\dagger})|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2}\Bigr)\rho(V^{\dagger})

(6.13b)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2}|Y^{\dagger}-\mathsf{h}(V^{\dagger})|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}|V^{\dagger}-\mathsf{\Psi}\bigl(V^{\dagger}_{J-1};m_{0}\bigr)|_{\mathsf{\Sigma}}^{2}\Bigr).

Remark 6.6
.

Because filtering is defined by conditioning on data arriving sequentially,
such algorithms may be used
online
, the probability distribution over states being updated every time
a new data point
y^{\dagger}_{j}
arrives. Smoothing gives rise to methodologies that are most
naturally used, in their most basic form, in an
offline
fashion.
However, smoothing algorithms may also be used sequentially, in block form with respect to discrete time index
j
, as we
now explain.

In the above we assumed data
y^{\dagger}_{j}
given on the
index set
j\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
and solved the smoothing problem on

j\in\{0,\ldots,J\}.
However this can be shifted to solve the inverse problem
on any interval, for example containing
K+1
points for some integer
K
, using data
on an interval excluding the first point, but provided with an initial Gaussian distribution
at the first point. Let
K\ll J
be such an integer. We first solve the
smoothing problem defined on the index set

j\in\{0,\ldots,K\}.

Taking the solution at time
K
as starting point we
may then solve a smoothing problem
on index set
j\in\{K,\ldots,2K\}.

This idea may be iterated, working on index set
j\in\{\ell K,\ldots,(\ell+1)K\}
, then on index set
j\in\{(\ell+1)K,\ldots,(\ell+2)K\}
and so on.
If
\ell=0,\dots,L-1
then this will deliver a solution to the data assimilation problem
for
v^{\dagger}_{j}
defined on
j\in\{0,\ldots,J\}
where
J=LK.

Such methods are known as
fixed-interval smoothers
and may be thought of as blending aspects
of smoothing and filtering, when iterated over
\ell.
At overlap points, which are a multiple
of
K
, some form of Gaussian projection (see Remark
2.13
) will be needed to restart the process on the next time interval as the assumption made above is that the initial condition is Gaussian; however, this assumption on the initial distribution can be relaxed.
The case
K=1
delivers the filtering distribution if the Gaussian projection is omitted.
∎

6.3
Filtering Algorithms

In this section we formulate various filtering algorithms.
Subsection
6.3.1
recalls the
Kalman filter, applicable in the linear and Gaussian setting, and in Subsection
6.3.2
we study the Kalman filter when the covariance is in steady state.
Subsections
6.3.3
,
6.3.4
,
6.3.5
and
6.3.6
recall 3DVar, the extended Kalman filter (ExKF), the unscented Kalman filter (UKF) and the ensemble Kalman filter (EnKF) respectively. Subsections
6.3.7
and
6.3.8
concern the bootstrap particle
filter and the optimal particle
filter approaches to filtering. We discuss
the issue of weight collapse, which can degrade performance of the
bootstrap and particle filters in high dimensions
d
, in Subsection
6.3.9
;
this degradation underpins the widespread adoption of the EnKF in
the high-dimensional setting as it is a particle filter
with equal weights.
We conclude the section, in Subsection
6.3.10
,
with discussion of a commonly used heuristic methodology for evaluating filtering algorithms.

The filtering distribution is, in general, an infinite dimensional object. In order
to approximate it (probabilistic estimation) some
form of finite-dimensionalization is needed. Most
approximation methods use either empirical particle or
Gaussian approximations, or a combination of both ideas.
The algorithms we study comprise nonautonomous dynamical systems,
defined on state spaces of varying dimension.
They are nonautonomous because they are driven by the observation
sequence
\{y^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
; the dimension varies depending on whether a
state estimate is evolved (3DVar), mean and covariance
are evolved (Kalman filter or the extended Kalman
filter), or an ensemble is evolved
(ensemble Kalman filter, bootstrap
particle filter, optimal particle filter).

6.3.1
Kalman Filter

Here we consider the dynamics and observation models (
6.1
) and (
6.2
) in which the dynamics and observation operators are linear; furthermore we generalize to make them non-autonomous:

(6.14)

\Psi(\cdot)\mapsto A_{j}\cdot\,,\quad h(\cdot)\mapsto H_{j}\cdot\,.

Then the solution to the filtering problem is Gaussian and
\pi_{j}=\mathcal{N}(v_{j},C_{j})
,

\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}=\mathcal{N}(\widehat{v}_{j+1},\widehat{C}_{j+1}).
The update rules for the
mean
3
3

3

We use symbol
v_{j}
for the mean here, and not
m_{j}
, because of the links with
the 3DVar algorithm, which uses
v_{j}
as state estimator; indeed, in the linear setting
of the Kalman filter considered in this subsection, it is natural to view the mean
v_{j}
as a state estimator. This is related to the discussion of posterior mean estimators
for general Bayesian inversion; see Example
1.16
.

v_{j}
and covariance
C_{j}
are given by the
Kalman filter
: the mean is initialized at
v_{0}=m_{0}
and then updated, for
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, according to

(6.15a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=A_{j}v_{j},

(6.15b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{j+1}+K_{j+1}\bigl(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-H_{j+1}\widehat{v}_{j+1}\bigr).

Here the
Kalman gain

K_{j+1}
is determined as part
of the following update rule
C_{j}\mapsto\widehat{C}_{j+1}\mapsto C_{j+1}
for the covariances

(6.16a)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=A_{j}C_{j}A_{j}^{\top}+\Sigma,

(6.16b)

\displaystyle S_{j+1}

\displaystyle=H_{j+1}\widehat{C}_{j+1}H_{j+1}^{\top}+\Gamma,

(6.16c)

\displaystyle K_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{j+1}H_{j+1}^{\top}S_{j+1}^{-1},

(6.16d)

\displaystyle C_{j+1}

\displaystyle=(I-K_{j+1}H_{j+1})\widehat{C}_{j+1}.

The gain is thus computed using the predictive covariance which, when suitably
combined with noise covariance and the observation operator, facilitates the transfer
of information from observation space to state space.
Indeed

K_{j+1}=\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{vy}\bigl(\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{yy}\bigr)^{-1},

where
\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{vy}:=\widehat{C}_{j+1}H_{j+1}^{\top}

is the predictive covariance between state and observation,
and
\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{yy}:=S_{j+1}
is the predictive covariance of the observation.

Together (
6.15
a) and (
6.16
a) define
a mapping from mean and covariance, at time
j
, to predictive mean and covariance,
at time
j+1
, representing the outcome of
the prediction step
{\mathsf{P}}

in (
6.4
):

(6.17)

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})=\mathcal{N}\bigl(v^{\dagger}_{j+1};\widehat{v}_{j+1},\widehat{C}_{j+1}\bigr).

The remaining equations (
6.15
b) and (
6.16
b)–(
6.16
d) then define the analysis step
{\mathsf{A}}_{j}
in (
6.4
):

(6.18)

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j+1})=\mathcal{N}\bigl(v^{\dagger}_{j+1};v_{j+1},C_{j+1}\bigr)

mapping the predictive mean and covariance at time
j+1
to the mean and covariance
of the filter at time
j+1.
Both
{\mathsf{P}}
and
{\mathsf{A}}_{j}
map Gaussians to
Gaussians in this setting.

The update for the covariance evolves independently of the update for the mean and is independent of the data; in contrast, the update for the mean depends on the update for the covariance
and on the data.
Once the covariance is known,
v_{j}
provides a state estimator for

v^{\dagger}_{j}
, given the specific observations
Y^{\dagger}_{j}
; the Kalman filter updates this estimator sequentially.
Furthermore, since the Gaussian
\mathcal{N}(v_{j},C_{j})
is actually equal to the filtering distribution
\pi_{j}

the methodology constitutes exact
probabilistic estimation.

6.3.2
Steady State Kalman Filter

In this subsection we consider the Kalman filter in the setting where

A_{j}=A
and
H_{j}=H
are constant in time. Then, under appropriate controllability and observability assumptions,
the covariance
converges to a unique steady state. The resulting
steady state covariance and gain can be obtained by finding the unique solution
(\widehat{C}_{\infty},S_{\infty},K_{\infty})
to the equations

(6.19a)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{\infty}

\displaystyle=A(I-K_{\infty}H)\widehat{C}_{\infty}A^{\top}+\Sigma,

(6.19b)

\displaystyle S_{\infty}

\displaystyle=H\widehat{C}_{\infty}H^{\top}+\Gamma,

(6.19c)

\displaystyle K_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{\infty}H^{\top}S_{\infty}^{-1},

and setting

(6.20)

C_{\infty}=(I-K_{\infty}H)\widehat{C}_{\infty}.

If we assume that
C_{0}=C_{\infty}
then,
using the steady-state Kalman gain from (
6.19
c) in the
update formulae (
6.15
) we obtain
state estimator update equations in the form

(6.21a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=Av_{j},

(6.21b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{j+1}+K_{\infty}\bigl(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-H\widehat{v}_{j+1}\bigr).

We note the form of this update of the mean
v_{j}\mapsto v_{j+1}
, viewed as a state estimator:
it comprises a prediction step
v_{j}\mapsto\widehat{v}_{j+1},
in the form of the stochastic dynamics model (
6.1
) with the noise dropped, and an analysis step
\widehat{v}_{j+1}\mapsto v_{j+1}
which incorporates data given by the data model (
6.2
).
Combining the prediction and data incorporation steps we obtain

(6.22)

v_{j+1}=(I-K_{\infty}H)Av_{j}+K_{\infty}y^{\dagger}_{j+1}.

Updating the mean according to this formula, and fixing the covariance at
C_{\infty}
is
sometimes referred to as the steady state Kalman filter.

6.3.3
3DVar

The 3DVar algorithm does not attempt to approximate the filtering distribution; rather
it just attempts to provide a state estimator. However aspects of the algorithm
can be motivated by the form of the steady-state Kalman filter
from the preceding subsection. To motivate 3DVar we consider the form of
the Kalman filter when the covariance is in steady state.
Motivated by the mean update equations (
6.21
)
for the steady state Kalman filter,
when the gain is in steady state, we propose the following generalization
to the setting of nonlinear
(\Psi,h):

(6.23a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j})+s\xi_{j},

(6.23b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{j+1}+K\bigl(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-h(\widehat{v}_{j+1})\bigr).

The algorithm is initialized at
v_{0}
, typically sampled from Gaussian

\mathcal{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
, the known distribution of
v^{\dagger}_{0}.

In the setting where
h(\cdot):=H\cdot
is linear
the algorithm can be written in the form

(6.24)

v_{j+1}=(I-KH)\Psi(v_{j})+Ky^{\dagger}_{j+1}+s(I-KH)\xi_{j}.

Remark 6.7
.

The standard form of (
6.23
) employs
s=0
and the resulting
algorithm should be viewed as a state estimator.
Note also that, when
s=0
and
\Psi(\cdot)=A\cdot
is linear, (
6.24
) recovers the
same form as the steady state Kalman mean update (
6.22
).
We have included the case
s=1
primarily so that we can make a connection
to the optimal particle filter introduced
in Subsection
6.3.8
. In that context we will discuss the distribution
of the i.i.d. sequence
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}.}

∎

Remark 6.8
.

The methodology encapsulated in (
6.23
) is in fact more properly termed
cycled 3DVar
.
In this context the
3DVar
component of the nomenclature refers to the analysis step (
6.23
b), which is
cycled
with the prediction step (
6.23
a) by iterating over
j.
For
linear observation operator we show below that the analysis step has an optimization, or
variational
, formulation; this is the source of the “Var” terminology. The use of “3D” refers to the fact that, in weather forecasting where the methodology was introduced, the optimization problem is for a field in three physical space dimensions.
∎

We conclude this discussion of 3DVar by formulating the analysis step via an optimization problem.
To appreciate this connection to optimization we consider the case where
\Psi
is allowed to be nonlinear, but
h(\cdot)=H\cdot
is linear. The predict-then-optimize viewpoint then leads to 3DVar above if
v_{j+1}
is computed from
(\widehat{v}_{j+1},y^{\dagger}_{j+1})
by solving the optimization problem

(6.25a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|v-\widehat{v}_{j+1}|^{2}_{\widehat{C}}+\frac{1}{2}|y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-h(v)|^{2}_{\Gamma},

(6.25b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(v).

Remark 6.9
.

The solution
v_{j+1}
of (
6.25
) is equivalent to the maximum
a posteriori
solution of the Bayesian inverse problem with prior
\rho(v)=\mathcal{N}(\widehat{v}_{j+1},\widehat{C})
, and likelihood
\mathsf{l}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v)=\mathcal{N}(h(v),\Gamma)
; see Example
1.14
.
∎

The minimization problem (
6.25
) delivers (
6.23
b), in the case
h(\cdot)=H\cdot
, provided that
K
in (
6.23
b) and
\widehat{C}
in (
6.25
a)
are related by

(6.26)

K=\widehat{C}H^{\top}(H\widehat{C}H^{\top}+\Gamma)^{-1}.

When
h(\cdot)
is nonlinear, an explicit solution of the optimization problem (
6.25
) is not available in closed form. However, numerical optimization may be used to find
v_{j+1}
from
\widehat{v}_{j+1}
and
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}.

Equation (
6.26
) should be compared with formula
(
6.19
a)–(
6.19
c) defining
the steady-state Kalman gain.
In particular this comparison suggests a methodology for choosing
K
in 3DVar: instead of
choosing
K
directly, choose an estimate of the uncertainty in the prediction,
\widehat{C}
, and use this to
define
K
from (
6.26
). This approach to modeling
\widehat{C}
, and then deducing
K
, is natural because the uncertainty in the prediction is an interpretable quantity.

6.3.4
Extended Kalman Filter

The Kalman filter from Subsection
6.3.1
gives an exact expression
for the filtering distribution, which is Gaussian, in the setting of linear dynamics and observation operators. It is structured around
formulae for the prediction and analysis updates defined in equation (
6.4
).
The extended Kalman filter (ExKF) generalizes the Kalman filter to nonlinear dynamics map
\Psi
and nonlinear observation map
h

by linearizing these functions, leading to
approximate
Gaussian
representation of the filtering distribution.
Specifically we seek the prediction and analysis
distributions as approximations of the form

(6.27)

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})\approx\mathcal{N}\bigl(v^{\dagger}_{j+1};\widehat{v}_{j+1},\widehat{C}_{j+1}\bigr)

for the predictive distribution; and

(6.28)

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j+1})\approx\mathcal{N}\bigl(v^{\dagger}_{j+1};v_{j+1},C_{j+1}\bigr)

for the analysis distribution.
We now seek update rules to map
(v_{j},C_{j})
to
(\widehat{v}_{j+1},\widehat{C}_{j+1})
, the
approximate prediction step; and to map
(\widehat{v}_{j+1},\widehat{C}_{j+1})
to

(v_{j+1},C_{j+1})
, the approximate analysis step. Doing this involves
computation of the extended Kalman filter gain.

The output
v_{j}
from ExKF provides state estimation for
v^{\dagger}_{j},
given
Y^{\dagger}_{j}.
The Gaussian
\mathcal{N}(v_{j},C_{j})
enables probabilistic estimation to be undertaken. For this reason we will
refer, for this algorithm, to
v_{j}
as the mean, as for the Kalman filter.
We define the Jacobians of the functions and evaluate them at the outputs
v_{j}
and
\widehat{v}_{j}
of a putative filtering algorithm:

(6.29a)

\displaystyle A_{j}

\displaystyle:=D\Psi(v_{j}),

(6.29b)

\displaystyle H_{j}

\displaystyle:=Dh(\widehat{v}_{j}).

These Jacobians may be known analytically; otherwise, auto-differentiation (Section
15.7
) can be used to obtain them.

The mean is initialized at
v_{0}=m_{0}
and the update is then performed using the nonlinear
\Psi
and
h
,
similarly to 3DVar (
6.23
) with
s=0
,
but with an evolving gain:

(6.30a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j}),

(6.30b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{j+1}+K_{j+1}\bigl(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-h(\widehat{v}_{j+1})\bigr).

The computation of
K_{j+1}
, and the covariances involved in its
definition, uses (
6.16
), but with evolving
(A_{j},H_{j})

defined by (
6.29
), (
6.30
):

(6.31a)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=A_{j}C_{j}A_{j}^{\top}+\Sigma,

(6.31b)

\displaystyle S_{j+1}

\displaystyle=H_{j+1}\widehat{C}_{j+1}H_{j+1}^{\top}+\Gamma,

(6.31c)

\displaystyle K_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{j+1}H_{j+1}^{\top}S_{j+1}^{-1},

(6.31d)

\displaystyle C_{j+1}

\displaystyle=(I-K_{j+1}H_{j+1})\widehat{C}_{j+1}.

As for the Kalman filter, the gain facilitates transfer of information from observation
space to state space.

Remark 6.10
.

The ExKF recovers the true filtering distribution in the case of linear
\Psi
and
h
, where it reduces to the standard Kalman filter from Subsection
6.3.1
. It is often used beyond the linear Gaussian setting:
for example when
\Psi
and
h
are close to linear; or when small covariances are assumed in both the dynamics and data models. In both these settings linearization may be used to justify use of the ExKF.
∎

6.3.5
Unscented Kalman Filter

The Kalman filter solves the filtering problem when the exact distribution on
state given observations is Gaussian; thus only a mean and covariance need
to be computed. The ExKF approximates the general filtering distribution by
a Gaussian, using linearization to derive the joint evolution of mean
and covariance. The unscented Kalman filter
(UKF)
also makes a Gaussian ansatz, but does not require
linearization of the dynamics and observation maps;
instead it uses the projection
{\mathsf{G}}
onto Gaussian measures, defined in Remark
2.13
.

The UKF algorithm may be written
in the following form, which approximates the exact prediction–analysis cycle from (
6.4
):

(6.32)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\text{ \bf UKF Prediction Step:}}~~~~\;\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}={\mathsf{P}}\pi_{j}.\\
&{\text{ \bf UKF Analysis Step:}}~~~~~\,\,\,\,\,\pi_{j+1}={\mathsf{U}}(\widehat{\pi}_{j+1};y^{\dagger}_{j+1}).\end{split}

Here, for
\gamma_{\pi}(u,y)
defined by (
6.7
),
we define the unscented approximation of the analysis operator
{\mathsf{A}}
by

(6.33)

{\mathsf{U}}(\pi;y^{\dagger})(u)=\frac{{\mathsf{G}}\gamma_{\pi}(u,y^{\dagger})}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathsf{G}}\gamma_{\pi}(u,y^{\dagger})\,du}.

The preceding gives an iteration with property that
\pi_{j}
is Gaussian
for all
j
, but
\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}
is not (unless
\Psi
is linear).
The algorithm thus produces an approximation of the filtering distribution
which remains in the manifold of Gaussians; it can hence
be represented in terms of propagation of means and covariances. As for the ExKF, it reduces to the Kalman filter if the dynamics and observation maps are linear. In practice the
integrations required to define the Gaussian projection are approximated by quadratures; it is the resulting method, after quadrature, that is typically referred to as the unscented Kalman filter.

6.3.6
Ensemble Kalman Filter (EnKF)

Despite the simplicity of the ExKF, computing and storing covariances when
d
or
k
is large can be prohibitively
expensive. This motivates the ensemble methods we describe next. These methods carry an ensemble of particles which are used to compute low-rank approximations of covariances which enable, via the resulting gain, transfer of information from the observations to the state. In this subsection our first, and primary, goal is to introduce the ensemble Kalman filter (EnKF) algorithm, a specific methodology of this type.
In addition, we also discuss inflation and localization, two
important practical modifications of the methodology.

The EnKF Algorithm

The content of Remark
6.8
, concerning 3DVar, is to shift
the problem of choosing
K
to one of choosing
\widehat{C}
, an estimate of the uncertainty in the predictions.
In the ExKF estimation of the uncertainty in predictions is achieved via a linearization. The EnKF instead forms a low-rank approximation of the covariance based on an ensemble in state space. The algorithm operates by running an ensemble of
N
interacting copies of 3DVar-like algorithms. These are
then used to estimate the filtering distribution, and means and covariances in particular. The covariances
are used to define interactions between the particles.

Specifically we seek an approximation of the filtering distribution in the form

(6.34)

\pi_{j}\approx\pi^{\mathrm{EnKF}}_{j}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{v_{j}^{(n)}}.

The prediction and analysis distributions are sought as approximations of the form

(6.35)

\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}\approx\widehat{\pi}^{\mathrm{EnKF}}_{j+1}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{\,\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}}

and

(6.36)

\pi_{j+1}\approx\pi^{\mathrm{EnKF}}_{j+1}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{v_{j+1}^{(n)}}.

We now describe the update rules for the prediction step
\{v_{j}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}\mapsto\{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
and the analysis
step
\{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}\mapsto\{v_{j+1}^{(n+1)}\}_{n=1}^{N},
approximating (
6.4
).
These updates will involve independent copies of the random variables
\xi^{\dagger}_{j}
and
\eta^{\dagger}_{j}
entering the signal and data models (
6.1
) and (
6.2
). Specifically,
we make the following assumption:

Assumption 6.11
.

Here
\xi_{j}^{(n)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma),\,\eta_{j+1}^{(n)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Gamma)

are independent arrays of i.i.d. random vectors with respect to both
j
and
n
, and
the two arrays themselves are independent of one another. The initial conditions for the
ensemble at
j=0
are also chosen independently from one another and from these i.i.d. arrays.

Unlike 3DVar, the updates for
\{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
employ a time-dependent gain
K_{j+1}
; this gain is estimated
by computing the empirical covariances of the predicted states

\{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
and

\{h\bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}\bigr)\}_{n=1}^{N}
,
their mappings under
h
. The algorithm takes the form

(6.37a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j}^{(n)})+\xi^{(n)}_{j},\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(6.37b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}+K_{j+1}\bigl(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\eta_{j+1}^{(n)}-h(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)})\bigr),\quad n=1,\ldots,N.

The gain matrix
K_{j+1}
is calculated according to

(6.38a)

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{n=1}\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},

(6.38b)

\displaystyle\widehat{h}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{n=1}h(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}),

(6.38c)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{vh}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{n=1}\bigl(\widehat{v}^{(n)}_{j+1}-\widehat{m}_{j+1}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(h(\widehat{v}^{(n)}_{j+1})-\widehat{h}_{j+1}\bigr),

(6.38d)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{hh}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{n=1}\bigl(h(\widehat{v}^{(n)}_{j+1})-\widehat{h}_{j+1}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(h(\widehat{v}^{(n)}_{j+1})-\widehat{h}_{j+1}\bigr),

(6.38e)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{yy}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{hh}+\Gamma,

(6.38f)

\displaystyle K_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{vh}\bigl(\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{yy}\bigr)^{-1}.

As well as the empirical approximation of the filtering distribution,
defined by (
6.34
), it is also possible, and indeed natural, to approximate
\pi_{j}
with a Gaussian computed
using empirical moments. Recall the notation
for
{\mathsf{G}}
, the projection onto Gaussian measures, defined in Remark
2.13
. Then note that

(6.39)

{\mathsf{G}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{v_{j}^{(n)}}\Bigr)=\mathcal{N}(m_{j},C_{j}),

where the ensemble mean
m_{j}
and covariance
C_{j}
are given by

(6.40)

{m}_{j}=\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{n=1}{v}_{j}^{(n)},\quad{C}_{j}=\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{n=1}\bigl({v}^{(n)}_{j}-{m}_{j}\bigr)\otimes\bigl({v}^{(n)}_{j}-{m}_{j}\bigr).

Whilst (
6.34
) provides probabilistic estimation,
mean
m_{j}
may be viewed as providing state estimation.

Remark 6.12
.

Conditions under which the EnKF provides a good approximation of
the true filtering distribution are discussed in the bibliography Section
6.8
, and revolve around Gaussian approximations. When the true filtering and predictive distributions are close to Gaussian then the EnKF
accurately approximates these distributions if sufficient particles are used.
When the true filtering distribution is far from Gaussian, however, the method will typically fail to
provide accurate probabilistic estimation. In this case, the EnKF can still provide accurate state estimation under observability conditions that ensure the data is sufficiently informative, as discussed in the bibliography Section
6.8
.
∎

Remark 6.13
.

We note that in the case where
h(\cdot)=H\cdot
(and is hence linear) we may calculate the gain through estimation of a single covariance, as follows:

(6.41a)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{n=1}\bigl(\widehat{v}^{(n)}_{j+1}-\widehat{m}_{j+1}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\widehat{v}^{(n)}_{j+1}-\widehat{m}_{j+1}\bigr),

(6.41b)

\displaystyle K_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{j+1}H^{\top}\bigl(H\widehat{C}_{j+1}H^{\top}+\Gamma\bigr)^{-1}.

This should be compared with the gain (
6.26
), resulting from 3DVar when applied in the linear case
h(\cdot)=H\cdot
.
The analysis mean and covariance can be estimated using (
6.40
) with index
j\mapsto j+1.

∎

Remark 6.14
.

The particle system (
6.37
) is sometimes referred to as the
perturbed
observation
form of the EnKF;
this is because of the presence of the noise

\eta^{(n)}_{j+1}
which is sometimes viewed by practitioners as perturbing the
observation
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
. We present an alternative
way of thinking about the algorithm, in terms of
simulated observations
. Equations (
6.37
) can be
written in the equivalent form

(6.42a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j}^{(n)})+\xi^{(n)}_{j},\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(6.42b)

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=h(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)})+\eta^{(n)}_{j+1},\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(6.42c)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}+K_{j+1}\,i_{j+1}^{(n)},\quad n=1,\ldots,N.

Here define the
innovation
by

(6.43)

i_{j+1}^{(n)}=y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)}.

This way of writing the EnKF leads to an interpretation of the
algorithm as predicting both the state
\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}
and the observation
\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)}
,
and then correcting the predicted state by a quantity linearly related to
the discrepancy between the observation
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
and the predicted observation.
∎

Remark 6.15
.

Consider the setting of linear
\Psi
and
h
. With the addition of the perturbations to the observations, as described in the preceding remark, the predictive and filter covariances in (
6.41a
) and (
6.40
) match those of the Kalman filter, in the large ensemble limit
N=\infty
. See the bibliography Section
6.8
.
∎

EnKFs are widely used in high-dimensional problems because they do not suffer from the weight
collapse that plagues the particle filters introduced in Subsection
6.3.7
.
Weight collapse is discussed in Subsection
6.3.9
.
We refer to EnKFs as
equal weights particle filters
.
However, EnKFs often need inflation and localization to perform well for high-dimensional systems, for chaotic dynamical systems, and for small ensemble sizes. Inflation and localization are discussed in the following two paragraphs.

Inflation

For simplicity we confine the discussion to the case of linear
observation operator
h(\cdot)=H\cdot.
The EnKF gain
is then determined by
\widehat{C}_{j+1}
, as defined in Remark
6.13
. Because we
are interested here in dependence on sample size we write
\widehat{C}_{j+1}(N).

We assume that the covariance at infinite sample size
N=\infty
,
\widehat{C}_{j+1}(\infty)
,
leads to the optimal choice of gain. This assumption is provably true,
utilizing a precise definition of “optimal”, in the case of linear
\Psi
; see the
discussion in the bibliography. From this perspective, the EnKF suffers
from sampling error due to finite ensemble size
N
.
When sampling error in the forecast covariance matrix
\widehat{C}_{j+1}(N)
leads to underestimation of the
optimal analysis covariance
C_{j+1},
this can lead to filter divergence, whereby repeated underestimation of the forecast covariance causes the filter to put increasingly more weight on the forecasts than observations, eventually becoming unresponsive to observations. We provide two examples to illustrate the underestimation of the analysis covariance.

Example 6.16
.

Suppose that we assimilate a scalar observation of the
b^{\text{th}}
variable into the
a^{\text{th}}
variable. Thus
H
is a
1\times d
vector with entry
1
in the
b^{\text{th}}
position and zeros elsewhere. Since
the phenomenon we wish to illustrate occurs at any fixed time, we simply let
C
and
\widehat{C}
represent the desired
infinite sample size covariances, and
C(N)
and
\widehat{C}(N)
the finite sample size estimators coming
from the EnKF. Now, we claim that

(6.44)

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\bigl({C}(N)\bigr)_{aa}]-\bigl(\widehat{C}(N)\bigr)_{aa}=\frac{-(\widehat{C}(N))_{ab}^{2}}{\bigl(\widehat{C}(N)\bigr)_{bb}+(\Gamma)_{bb}},

where the expectation is taken with respect to the observation perturbations
\eta_{j}^{(n)}
. References that substantiate this claim can be found in the bibliography Section
6.8
. Next, suppose that variables with indices
a
and
b
are in fact uncorrelated, so that
\bigl(\widehat{C}\bigr)_{ab}=0.
When estimated with a finite ensemble, in an implementation of the EnKF, the estimated
\bigl(\widehat{C}(N)\bigr)_{ab}\neq 0
, due to sampling error resulting from a finite ensemble. This leads to a spurious reduction in the analysis covariance, with respect to its optimal value that is defined by the infinite-ensemble case.
∎

Example 6.17
.

Inflation is also used to compensate for an unknown, or ignored,
\Sigma
, a form of model error. If the
\xi^{(n)}_{j}
are excluded in
(
6.37a
), then the covariance

\widehat{C}_{j+1}
will be underestimated in (
6.41a
).
∎

The underestimation of the analysis covariance can be mitigated by inflating the covariance of the forecast ensemble, although inflation can also be applied to the analysis ensemble instead. The most common form of inflation is
multiplicative inflation
, whereby the forecast ensemble is modified as

(6.45)

\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}\to\widehat{m}_{j+1}+\alpha(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}-\widehat{m}_{j+1}),

with
\alpha\geq 1
being the inflation parameter. This corresponds to scaling the covariance
\widehat{C}_{j+1}
by
\alpha^{2}
.
Theoretical results on the impact of sampling error on EnKFs, including results on the optimal value of the inflation parameter under assumptions on the model dynamics, are referenced in the bibliography Section
6.8
.

Localization

In addition to the undersampling effects from the previous paragraph,
further negative impacts of sampling errors on EnKFs can arise in spatially extended systems. In such physical systems there is typically decay of correlations with distance; but sampling error can induce spurious long-range correlations. In the empirical covariance matrices computed by the EnKFs, nearby points in space are likely to be truly correlated, while ones that are farther apart may only have spurious correlations dominated by sampling error. These spurious correlations not only lead to an underestimation of the analysis covariance, as discussed in Example
6.16
, but also lead to degraded state estimation due to spurious information transfer between uncorrelated variables.

Localization
is introduced to damp covariances, within the empirical covariance matrices computed by the EnKF, according to physical distance
(for discretization of PDE problems) or index distance (for large ODEs with notion of index locality). A common way of implementing localization is by taking the Hadamard product
4
4

4

The Hadamard
product computes the product of two matrices
of the same dimensions elementwise; it is also known as the Schur product.
of the empirical covariance with a localization matrix
L
:

(6.46)

\widehat{C}_{j+1}\to L\circ\widehat{C}_{j+1}.

Typically,
L
is constructed such that the covariance between variables indexed
by integers
a
and
b
is exponentially damped according to a measure of
distance
5
5

5

Computed, for example, using a deterministic scoring rule from Definition
11.81
.

\mathsf{d}(a,b)
between them, scaled by characteristic length scale
\ell
:

(6.47)

(L)_{ab}=e^{-\mathsf{d}(a,b)^{2}/\ell^{2}}.

The choice of
\ell
will depend on the ensemble size, the correlation scales in the system, and the time between analysis steps (since this will control how far information has had time to propagate). Localization can also be implemented in other ways that scale better to high-dimensional problems; these include domain localization and observation localization, discussed in the bibliography Section
6.8
. In the main body of the text,
we restrict our discussion to covariance localization (
6.46
).

Remark 6.18
.

If
h(\cdot)
is linear, the localized
\widehat{C}_{j+1}
is used to compute the
gain according to (
6.41b
). If
h(\cdot)
is nonlinear, then localization needs to be applied to both
\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{vh}
and
\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{hh}
to compute the gain according to (
6.38
f). If function
h(\cdot)
is such that each observation has a corresponding spatial location, then localization can be applied as discussed above. If some observations correspond to, for example, spatially integrated quantities, then localization is not straightforward to apply; see the bibliography
Section
6.8
.
∎

Remark 6.19
.

Besides the sampling error considerations discussed above, localization is also important in increasing the rank of the forecast covariance matrix. When the ensemble size
N
is less than the system dimension
d
, the forecast covariance will be rank deficient. This implies that the analysis ensemble will lie in the span of the forecast ensemble, and thus that analysis increments are restricted to an
N
-dimensional subspace. Localization typically increases the rank of the forecast covariance, bypassing this subspace property.
∎

6.3.7
Bootstrap Particle Filter

The starting point for the bootstrap particle filter (BPF) is the factorization
(
6.4
) of the filtering update into two steps: prediction composed with analysis. We summarize this factorization here:

(6.48)

\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}={\mathsf{P}}\pi_{j},\,\,\,\,\,\pi_{j+1}={\mathsf{A}}_{j}(\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}).

The method starts with an empirical approximation to the filtering distribution

\pi_{j}\approx\pi^{\mathrm{BPF}}_{j}
. Here
\pi^{\mathrm{BPF}}_{0}=\pi_{0}=\mathcal{N}(m_{0},C_{0})

and, for
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

(6.49)

\pi^{\mathrm{BPF}}_{j}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}w_{j}^{(n)}\delta_{\widehat{v}_{j}^{(n)}}.

To define this approximation we evolve the particles
\widehat{v}_{j}^{(n)}

(the prediction step) and the weights
w_{j}^{(n)}
(the analysis step).
This is done according to the following update formulae, which hold for
n=1,\ldots,N
:

(6.50a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\Psi\bigl(v_{j}^{(n)}\bigr)+\xi_{j}^{(n)},\qquad v_{j}^{(n)}\overset{\rm i.i.d.}{\sim}\pi^{\mathrm{BPF}}_{j},

(6.50b)

\displaystyle\ell_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{2}\bigl\lvert y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-h\bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}\bigr)\bigr\rvert_{\Gamma}^{2}}}\right),

(6.50c)

\displaystyle w_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\ell_{j+1}^{(n)}\Big/\Bigl(\sum_{m=1}^{N}\ell_{j+1}^{(m)}\Bigr).

Here
\xi_{j}^{(n)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
are Gaussian random variables,
i.i.d. with respect to both
n
and
j,
generalizing the Assumption
6.1

concerning the underlying signal–observation model.

With this we obtain

(6.51)

\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}\sim{\mathsf{P}}\pi^{\mathrm{BPF}}_{j},\,\text{i.i.d.\quad and}\quad\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{1}{N}\delta_{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}}\approx{\mathsf{P}}\pi^{\mathrm{BPF}}_{j}.

The approximation error in this Monte Carlo approximation of
{\mathsf{P}}\pi^{\mathrm{BPF}}_{j}
may be quantified using Theorem
11.34
. We may then note that

(6.52)

\pi^{\mathrm{BPF}}_{j+1}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}w_{j+1}^{(n)}\delta_{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}}={\mathsf{A}}_{j}\Bigl(\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{1}{N}\delta_{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}}\Bigr)\approx{\mathsf{A}}_{j}\bigl({\mathsf{P}}\pi^{\mathrm{BPF}}_{j}\bigr).

Notice that the approximation in (
6.52
) arises from applying Bayes formula (the analysis map
{\mathsf{A}}_{j}
) with a Monte Carlo approximation of the prior (here
{\mathsf{P}}\pi^{\mathrm{BPF}}_{j}
). Thus, the error in this approximation can be quantified using Theorem
3.5
in conjunction with Theorem
11.34
.
The resulting algorithm that implements the update
\pi^{\mathrm{BPF}}_{j}\mapsto\pi^{\mathrm{BPF}}_{j+1}
is known as the BPF. It performs
probabilistic estimation: (
6.49
)
delivers an approximation
\pi^{\mathrm{BPF}}_{j}
to the true filtering distribution
\pi_{j}.

Remark 6.20
.

The BPF
is provably convergent to the true filtering distribution
as
N\to\infty
. In particular this convergence holds for
filtering distributions that are far from Gaussian. This fact should be contrasted
with ensemble methods as discussed in Remark
6.12
– they only provide accurate probabilistic estimation in the near-Gaussian setting. On the other hand, the BPF behaves poorly in many high-dimensional problems – see Subsection
6.3.9
.
∎

6.3.8
Optimal Particle Filter

The optimal particle filter (OPF) is also a probabilistic
estimation methodology and like the BPF it is based
on a particle approximation of the filter
\pi_{j}.
The OPF differs from the BPF
by reversing the order of the Bayesian inference step and the sampling from a Markovian kernel based on the dynamics.
It is directly implementable when the observation operator
h(\cdot)
is linear. We describe
the general formulation, followed by details in the linear observation setting.

OPF: General Setting

The starting point to understand the OPF is the following identity:

(6.53)

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j+1})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j+1})\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j+1})\,dv^{\dagger}_{j}.

Recall that
\pi_{j}=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j}).
Now note that, using Bayes Theorem
1.3
and Assumption
6.1

(6.54)

\mathfrak{p}_{j+1}:=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j+1})=\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j})}{\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})=\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v^{\dagger}_{j})}{\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})=:\mathsf{A}_{j}^{\textrm{OPF}}(\pi_{j}).

Note also that, using again Assumption
6.1
,
which defines the structure of a hidden Markov model, we have

(6.55)

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j+1})=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v^{\dagger}_{j},y^{\dagger}_{j+1}).

These identities show that we may factorize
the filtering update into:
(i)
\pi_{j}\mapsto\mathfrak{p}_{j+1}
, defined by (
6.54
);
and (ii)
\mathfrak{p}_{j+1}\mapsto\pi_{j+1}
, where

(6.56)

\pi_{j+1}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v,y^{\dagger}_{j+1})\mathfrak{p}_{j+1}(v)\,dv=:\mathsf{P}_{j}^{\textrm{OPF}}\mathfrak{p}_{j+1}.

The two steps in this factorization may be summarized as

(6.57)

\mathfrak{p}_{j+1}=\mathsf{A}_{j}^{\textrm{OPF}}(\pi_{j}),\quad\pi_{j+1}=\mathsf{P}_{j}^{\textrm{OPF}}\mathfrak{p}_{j+1}.

Note that both steps depend on the data
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
.
This factorization is the basis of the OPF.
The factorization should be compared with (
6.48
), the factorization underlying the
BPF.

Assume that we have a particle approximation of the true filter at time
j
,
in the form

(6.58)

\pi^{\mathrm{OPF}}_{j}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}w_{j}^{(n)}\delta_{\widehat{v}_{j}^{(n)}}.

We wish to update the weights and particles to mimic the two steps in the factorization
(
6.57
). To update the weights we proceed as follows

(6.59a)

\displaystyle v_{j}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\sim\pi^{\mathrm{OPF}}_{j}\,\,\mathrm{i.i.d.}\,,

(6.59b)

\displaystyle w_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\propto\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v_{j}^{(n)}),

where the weights
w_{j+1}^{(n)}
are normalized to sum to
1
over
n,

defining the constant of proportionality. We then have

(6.60)

\sum_{n=1}^{N}w_{j+1}^{(n)}\delta_{v_{j}^{(n)}}\approx{\mathsf{A}}_{j}^{\textrm{OPF}}(\pi^{\mathrm{OPF}}_{j}),

and we have mimicked step (i) in the factorization
(
6.57
). To mimic step (ii)
we define updated particles

\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v_{j}^{(n)},y^{\dagger}_{j+1})\,\,\mathrm{i.i.d.}\,,

and note that then

(6.61)

\pi^{\mathrm{OPF}}_{j+1}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}w_{j+1}^{(n)}\delta_{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}}\approx{\mathsf{P}}_{j}^{\textrm{OPF}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}w_{j+1}^{(n)}\delta_{v_{j}^{(n)}}.

Similarly to the BPF, the Monte Carlo approximation may be quantified using Theorem
11.34
.
Together (
6.60
) and (
6.61
) approximate (
6.57
).

OPF: Linear Observation Operator

In general nonlinear settings, it may not be possible to implement the OPF exactly. This arises for two reasons. First, the likelihood weights in (
6.59
b) are naturally expressed in terms of the integral

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v_{j}^{(n)})=\int\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v^{\dagger}_{j+1})\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v_{j}^{(n)})\,dv^{\dagger}_{j+1},

but this may not have a closed form. Second, sampling exactly from

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v_{j}^{(n)},y^{\dagger}_{j+1})
, as required to define (
6.61
), may not be possible.
However, one
(frequently occurring) setting where it is tractable to evaluate the likelihood weights and sample from
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v_{j}^{(n)},y^{\dagger}_{j+1})
is when the observation model is linear:
h(\cdot)=H\cdot
for some
H\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
. The state and observation models (
6.1
) and (
6.2
)
then reduce to the form

(6.62a)

\displaystyle v^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v^{\dagger}_{j})+\xi^{\dagger}_{j},

(6.62b)

\displaystyle y^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=Hv^{\dagger}_{j+1}+\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}\,.

We make the same Assumptions
6.1
and
6.2
as made for equations (
6.1
) and (
6.2
).
In the algorithm we are about to define
the covariance
C
, gain
K
and matrix
S
are defined by

(6.63a)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=(I-KH)\Sigma,

(6.63b)

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle=\Sigma H^{\top}S^{-1},

(6.63c)

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=H\Sigma H^{\top}+\Gamma.

Note that then

y^{\dagger}_{j+1}=H\Psi(v^{\dagger}_{j})+\zeta^{\dagger}_{j+1},

where
\zeta^{\dagger}_{j+1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,S).

Using that
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v_{j}^{(n)})
and
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v_{j}^{(n)},y^{\dagger}_{j+1})
are Gaussian
leads to the following OPF algorithm. First set
\pi^{\mathrm{OPF}}_{0}=\pi_{0}=\mathcal{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
.
Then recall, for
j\in\mathbb{N}
, the desired approximate filtering distribution
\pi^{\mathrm{OPF}}_{j}
given by (
6.58
). The particles
\widehat{v}_{j}^{(n)}
and weights
w_{j}^{(n)}
evolve according to, for
\zeta_{j+1}^{(n)}\sim\text{i.i.d.}\thinspace\mathcal{N}(0,C)
, and for
n=1,\ldots,N
,

(6.64a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=(I-KH)\Psi(v_{j}^{(n)})+Ky^{\dagger}_{j+1}+\zeta_{j+1}^{(n)},\qquad v_{j}^{(n)}\overset{\rm i.i.d.}{\sim}\pi^{\mathrm{OPF}}_{j},

(6.64b)

\displaystyle\ell_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{2}|y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-H\Psi(v_{j}^{(n)})|_{S}^{2}}}\right),

(6.64c)

\displaystyle w_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\ell_{j+1}^{(n)}/\sum_{m=1}^{N}\ell_{j+1}^{(m)}.

Now define
\pi^{\mathrm{OPF}}_{j+1}
according to (
6.61
), set
j\mapsto j+1
, and repeat the above steps.
The map from (
6.58
) to (
6.61
) approximates the true filter
update (
6.5
). We again emphasize that the ensemble of predictions (
6.64
a)
are 3DVar-like; see Remark
6.7
. Thus the complete
algorithm is a form of ensemble 3DVar.

Remark 6.21
.

Optimality
here refers to minimizing the variance
of the weights, in one step of the filter, over a wide
class of possible particle-based methods. Once
cycled through multiple steps
j
, this optimality property
is lost. Nonetheless the approach of using the data to predict is
natural. Indeed the OPF has a clear potential advantage over the BPF: the predictive kernel in the OPF,
\mathfrak{p}_{j+1}
, incorporates knowledge of the current observation
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
. In contrast, the predictive kernel in the BPF simply predicts with the unconditioned forecast kernel
{\mathsf{P}},
drawing samples from
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v_{j+1}|v_{j})
without knowledge of
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
, and then reweights
them to reflect the observation.
∎

6.3.9
Weight Collapse for Bootstrap and Optimal Particle Filters

The BPF can perform poorly in high dimensions,
suffering from weight collapse, a phenomenon whereby, for
j
large, all weights

\{w_{j}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}

are close to zero, with the exception of one weight close to one. As a consequence
the ensemble approximation
\pi^{\mathrm{BPF}}_{j}
is dominated by one particle. Although the OPF obviates this slightly by
minimizing weight variance, the problem still persists. Ensemble methods, on the other hand,
have been designed to perform robustly in high dimensions, and have equal weights on all
particles; again see Remark
6.12
for further discussion of
methods used to impart robustness on the EnKF in high dimensions.

On the other hand, the BPF and OPF are provably convergent to the true
filtering distribution, as the number of particles
N\to\infty.
In
contrast the EnKF and UKF are only provably convergent in the linear
Gaussian setting. These convergence issues are discussed in the bibliography
Section
6.8
. In Subsections
9.3.2
and
9.3.3
we propose a methodology
which aims to improve the accuracy of equal weights particle filters, with
respect to the true filtering distribution, using ideas from the BPF and the OPF.

6.3.10
Evaluating Probabilistic Estimation

In this subsection we describe two widely used methodologies for
evaluating probabilistic estimation of the filtering distribution, the spread–error ratio and rank histograms. The approximate probability distributions, and the corresponding true trajectory, are assumed to be available, as per the following Data Assumption.

Data Assumption 6.22
.

Assume we have a sequence of probabilistic forecasts
\{\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
generated using an approximate filtering methodology, along with the corresponding true trajectory
\{v^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{J}
generated from (
6.1
).

Remark 6.23
.

Typically the probabilistic forecasts will have used observations
\{y^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
, from (
6.2
), generated
in concert with
\{v^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{J}
from (
6.1
), but we do not make this explicit in Data Assumption
6.22
as the methodology for
evaluating
probabilistic forecasts does not require it. However, the optimality properties that underpin the evaluation methodologies that we now describe refer to the true filter which will have used
\{y^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
. For this reason, of course, any reasonable approximate filter will also have used

\{y^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
.
∎

Spread–Error Ratio

The use of the
spread–error ratio
as a diagnostic of probabilistic calibration is based on the following theorem which
shows that the filtering distribution has an
interesting property: its variance, averaged over the marginal distribution on
observations, equals the squared bias in its mean,
averaged over the joint distribution of the state and observations.

Theorem 6.24
.

In the following, the pair
(v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j})
is distributed according
to the joint distribution under the dynamics/data model (
6.1
), (
6.2
), with expectation denoted
\mathbb{E}^{(v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j})}
, and the expectation under the marginal distribution for
Y^{\dagger}_{j}
is denoted by
\mathbb{E}^{Y^{\dagger}_{j}}
. Furthermore,

v_{j}
is distributed according to the
filtering distribution for
v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j},
with
expectation denoted by
\mathbb{E}
. Then

\frac{\mathbb{E}^{Y^{\dagger}_{j}}\mathbb{E}\Bigl|v_{j}-\mathbb{E}v_{j}\Bigr|^{2}}{\mathbb{E}^{(v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j})}\Bigl|v^{\dagger}_{j}-\mathbb{E}v_{j}\Bigr|^{2}}=1.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the properties of conditional probability,
using that
\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j})
can be factored as

\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})\mathbb{P}(Y^{\dagger}_{j}).
Thus

\mathbb{E}^{(v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j})}\Bigl|v^{\dagger}_{j}-\mathbb{E}v_{j}\Bigr|^{2}=\mathbb{E}^{Y^{\dagger}_{j}}\Bigl(\mathbb{E}^{v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j}}\Bigl|v^{\dagger}_{j}-\mathbb{E}v_{j}\Bigr|^{2}\Bigr).

But, since
v_{j}
is distributed according to the
conditional
v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j},
for frozen
Y^{\dagger}_{j}
we have

\mathbb{E}^{v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j}}\Bigl|v^{\dagger}_{j}-\mathbb{E}v_{j}\Bigr|^{2}=\mathbb{E}\Bigl|v_{j}-\mathbb{E}v_{j}\Bigr|^{2}.

Thus the result follows.
∎

Remark 6.25
.

The preceding statement in Theorem
6.24
, and method of proof, can be generalized to the smoothing distribution
\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger})
if
Y^{\dagger}_{j}
is replaced by
Y^{\dagger}
.
∎

The result above motivates the use of the spread–error ratio,
which is defined next, to study probabilistic predictions.

Definition 6.26
.

Consider a sequence of probabilistic forecasts
\{\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
, as given by Data Assumption
6.22
.
The
spread–error ratio
of this
sequence with respect to the true trajectory
\{v^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
is the ratio

(6.65)

r:=\frac{\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{E}^{v_{j}\sim\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}}\bigl[|v_{j}-\mathbb{E}^{v_{j}\sim\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}}[v_{j}]|^{2}\bigr]}{\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\bigl|v^{\dagger}_{j}-\mathbb{E}^{v_{j}\sim\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}}[v_{j}]\bigr|^{2}}.

∎

Remark 6.27
.

This terminology for the spread–error ratio is used because
the denominator is the square
error
of the mean under
the filtering distribution, with respect to the true signal underlying
the data; the numerator is
the variance under the filtering distribution (the
spread
). The ratio in Theorem
6.24
averages over the marginal on the data and over the joint distribution of the state and observation. The spread–error ratio, however, replaces these expectations by averaging over the observations
along a single trajectory; this reflects the fact that only a single trajectory is available in practice. Nonetheless, the ratio serves as a heuristic for evaluating an algorithm.
If
r<1
, we say that the approximate filtering distributions
\{\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
are
underdispersed
. If
r>1
, we say that they are
overdispersed
. Ratios other than
1
can also be used, reflecting the heuristic derivation of
r
as an approximation of the exact value
1
that is obtained
in Theorem
6.24
.
∎

Remark 6.28
.

Another common metric for evaluating the spread–error relationship, which can also be motivated using Theorem
6.24
, is the spread–error correlation, computed as the sample Pearson correlation coefficient between the
spreads
(the terms of the summation in the numerator of (
6.65
)) and
errors
(the terms of the summation in the denominator of (
6.65
)). If the correlation coefficient is 1, we say that the approximate filtering distributions
\{\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
have a
perfect spread–error relationship
.
∎

Rank Histograms

Another common methodology for evaluating approximate filters is the rank histogram. We motivate this methodology by the following theorem.

Theorem 6.29
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a scalar function. Assume that pair
(v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j})
is distributed according
to the joint distribution under the dynamics/data model (
6.1
), (
6.2
). Then,
\operatorname{rank}(\{f(v^{(n)}_{j})\}_{n=1}^{N},f(v^{\dagger}_{j}))
, where
\{v^{(n)}_{j}\}_{n=1}^{N}
are drawn from the filtering distribution
\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})
, is uniformly distributed over the integers
[[0,N]]
.

Proof.

Replacing
u
with
v_{j}
and
y
with
Y_{j}
, the proof follows directly from Theorem
1.26
.
∎

This theorem suggests the following methodology.

Definition 6.30
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a scalar function. The
rank histogram
of a series of ensemble forecasts
\{\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
, where
\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{v^{(n)}}
, is the histogram of the rank values

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\operatorname{rank}(\{f(v_{j}^{(n)})\}_{n=1}^{N},f(v^{\dagger}_{j}))}}\right\}_{j=1}^{J}
.
∎

By Theorem
6.29
, the rank of the true trajectory with respect to samples from the true filtering distribution is uniformly distributed. Thus, deviation of the rank histogram from uniformity can indicate probabilistic miscalibration.

6.4
Smoothing Algorithms

In this section we describe a variety of smoothing algorithms.
Subsection
6.4.1
introduces the Rauch–Tung–Striebel smoother, the solution to the smoothing problem in the linear and Gaussian setting. Subsection
6.4.2
is devoted to the 4DVar approach
to smoothing, Subsection
6.4.3
discusses the strong constraint variant, Subsection
6.4.4
discusses the ensemble Kalman smoother, and the subject of reanalysis is overviewed in
Subsection
6.4.5
.

6.4.1
Rauch–Tung–Striebel Smoother

As in Subsection
6.3.1
,
here we consider the dynamics and observations models (
6.1
) and (
6.2
) in which the dynamics and observation operator are linear, and generalize to make them non-autonomous, so that
(
6.14
), repeated here for convenience, holds:

\Psi(\cdot)\mapsto A_{j}\cdot,\quad h(\cdot)\mapsto H_{j}\cdot\,.

Then the marginals of the smoothing distribution, at each time
j
, are Gaussian:
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger})=\mathcal{N}(v_{j}^{s},C_{j}^{s})
. Since the
j
-marginals of the filter and smoother
agree at
j=J
we also have
v_{J}^{s}=v_{J}
and
C_{J}^{s}=C_{J},
where
v_{J}
and
C_{J}
are the end-time mean and covariance given by the Kalman filter from Subsection
6.3.1
.

The
Rauch–Tung–Striebel (RTS) smoother
computes the means and covariances of these marginal smoothing distributions recursively by first carrying out a forward pass from
j=1
to
j=J
using the Kalman filter (Subsection
6.3.1
), and subsequently a backward pass to update the distribution at indices from
J-1
down to
0
. The backward pass modifies the Kalman filter solutions at time
j
by introducing the influence of future observations at times
j^{\prime}>j
.

The backward pass starts at
j=J
and uses the
\{v_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
,
\{\widehat{v}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
,
\{C_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
, and
\{\widehat{C}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
computed by the Kalman filter to compute the smoothed means and covariances, decreasing
j
from
J-1
to
0.
The
resulting algorithm has form

(6.66a)

\displaystyle v_{J}^{s}

\displaystyle=v_{J},\quad C_{J}^{s}=C_{J},

(6.66b)

\displaystyle v_{j}^{s}

\displaystyle=v_{j}+G_{j}(v_{j+1}^{s}-\widehat{v}_{j+1}),

(6.66c)

\displaystyle C_{j}^{s}

\displaystyle=C_{j}+G_{j}(C_{j+1}^{s}-\widehat{C}_{j+1})G_{j}^{\top},

(6.66d)

\displaystyle G_{j}

\displaystyle=C_{j}A_{j}^{\top}\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{-1}.

6.4.2
4DVar

We introduce the 4DVar methodology, a nomenclature which
abbreviates the specific
weak-constraint 4DVar
formulation that we focus on in this subsection. In short,
this method computes a MAP estimator (see Definition
1.10
) for the smoothing problem from Subsection
6.2.2
.
It is a state estimator.

To describe the methodology we recall the notation
V^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{d(J+1)},V^{\dagger}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d(j+1)}
and
Y^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{kJ}
from (
6.3
).
Here
V^{\dagger}:=\{v^{\dagger}_{0},\ldots,v^{\dagger}_{J}\}
is the dummy variable used in defining our optimization
problem to estimate the state. We then set

(6.67a)

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}(V^{\dagger})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}|v^{\dagger}_{0}-m_{0}|^{2}_{C_{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}|v^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\Psi(v^{\dagger}_{j})|^{2}_{\Sigma}

(6.67b)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|V^{\dagger}-\mathsf{\Psi}\bigl(V^{\dagger}_{J-1};m_{0}\bigr)|_{\mathsf{\Sigma}}^{2},

where the second expression involving
\mathsf{\Psi}
is defined in the text preceding (
6.11
), defining the prior for the smoothing problem. We also define

(6.68a)

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}(V^{\dagger};Y^{\dagger})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle{\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}}|y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-h(v^{\dagger}_{j+1})|_{\Gamma}^{2}

(6.68b)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|Y^{\dagger}-\mathsf{h}(V^{\dagger})|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2},

where the second expression involving
\mathsf{h}
is defined in the text preceding (
6.12
), defining the likelihood
for the smoothing problem. We may add the expressions in (
6.67
) and (
6.68
) to obtain

(6.69)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(V^{\dagger};Y^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{R}(V^{\dagger})+\mathsf{L}(V^{\dagger};Y^{\dagger}),

noting that
\exp\bigl(-{\mathsf{J}}(V^{\dagger};Y^{\dagger})\bigr)
is proportional to the posterior density
given in (
6.9
). The MAP estimator for this posterior is given by

(6.70)

V_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}\in\argmin_{V^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{d(J+1)}}{\mathsf{J}}(V^{\dagger};Y^{\dagger}).

We then take
V_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}
as our estimate of
V^{\dagger}.

In the linear-Gaussian setting this recovers the mean of the RTS smoother.

Remark 6.31
.

Minimization (
6.70
) is often implemented using Newton
or Gauss–Newton methodologies (see Section
15.3
). Ensemble approximations of Gauss–Newton
are also used – see Remark
15.23
. The problem (
6.70
) is the weak-constraint 4DVar method. The use of “4D” refers to the fact that, in weather
forecasting, the optimization problem is for a field in three physical
space dimensions and one time dimension; this should be contrasted with the reasoning for the nomenclature described in Remark
6.8
for 3DVar. As in 3DVar, the “Var” refers to the
optimization framing of the methodology. Weak constraint can be understood by comparing
with strong-constraint 4DVar in the next subsection.

As with all MAP estimators, the result of applying 4DVar is simply a point estimator. Obtaining additional information about the posterior with density proportional to
\exp\bigl(-{\mathsf{J}}(V^{\dagger};Y^{\dagger})\bigr)
requires the use of methods such as Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) and sequential Monte Carlo (SMC), or variational approximations; for the latter, see Section
7.4
. Hybrid ensemble–4DVar methods are also used to give uncertainty estimates.
∎

6.4.3
Strong-Constraint 4DVar

Strong-constraint 4DVar is found by letting
\Sigma\to 0
in (
6.70
). This results in a minimization problem defined with respect to
variable
v^{\dagger}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},
the initial condition of the deterministic dynamics model
arising from (
6.1
) by setting
\Sigma=0.
We then obtain point estimator

(6.71)

v_{0,\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}\in\argmin_{v^{\dagger}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathsf{J}}_{0}(v^{\dagger}_{0};Y^{\dagger}).

Here

(6.72)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}_{0}(v^{\dagger}_{0};Y^{\dagger})

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{R}_{0}(v^{\dagger}_{0})+\mathsf{L}_{0}(v^{\dagger}_{0};Y^{\dagger}),

where

(6.73)

\mathsf{R}_{0}(v^{\dagger}_{0}):=\frac{1}{2}|v^{\dagger}_{0}-m_{0}|^{2}_{C_{0}}

and

(6.74)

\mathsf{L}_{0}(v^{\dagger}_{0};Y^{\dagger}):=\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle{\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}}\big|y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-h\bigl(\Psi^{(j+1)}(v^{\dagger}_{0})\bigr)\big|_{\Gamma}^{2}.

In the preceding definition
\Psi^{(j)}
is the
j
-fold composition of
\Psi
with itself.
Strong-constraint 4DVar takes
v_{0}
as point estimate of
v^{\dagger}_{0};
it is a state
estimator. Other techniques, such as MCMC or SMC sampling algorithms, can be used for probabilistic estimation of the posterior on
v^{\dagger}_{0}|Y^{\dagger},
which has density proportional to
\exp\bigl(-{\mathsf{J}}_{0}(v^{\dagger}_{0};Y^{\dagger})\bigr).

Remark 6.32
.

The nomenclature
strong constraint
, as opposed to
weak constraint
, comes from the fact that the solution of (
6.71
) can then be used to initialize the deterministic model, found from (
6.1
) with
\Sigma=0
, leading to sequence
v_{j}=\Psi^{(j)}(v_{0})
. Then sequence
v_{j}
is said to be “strongly constrained” by the deterministic model. In contrast the trajectory in (
6.70
) is not constrained to satisfy the deterministic model. Strong-constraint 4DVar is appealing when the deterministic model conserves certain quantities, or has qualitative attributes, that the stochastic model does not share.
∎

6.4.4
Ensemble Kalman Smoother (EnKS)

The
ensemble Kalman smoother (EnKS)
is an ensemble extension of the Rauch–Tung–Striebel smoother (RTS smoother, Subsection
6.4.1
), which can be applied to nonlinear and non-Gaussian settings. This is similar to how the ensemble Kalman filter (EnKF, Subsection
6.3.6
) extends the Kalman filter and can similarly be applied beyond the linear Gaussian setting. Here, the task is to obtain at each time
j
an ensemble which approximates the marginal smoothing distribution; that is,

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger})\approx\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{{v}_{j}^{(n),\text{s}}}(v^{\dagger}_{j}),

where
v_{j}^{(n),\text{s}}
denotes the
n
th smoothed ensemble member for time
j
. Furthermore, the sequence of smoothed states from time
j=0
to
j=J
, for ensemble index
n
, is an approximate sample from the joint smoothing distribution; that is,

(6.75)

\{v_{j}^{(n),\text{s}}\}_{j=0}^{J}\underset{\footnotesize\text{approx.}}{\sim}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(V^{\dagger}|Y^{\dagger}).

As with the RTS smoother, the EnKS solution can be computed by carrying out a forward and subsequently a backward pass. The forward pass is defined by the EnKF: equation
(
6.37
) defines ensembles
\{v^{(n)}_{j}\}_{n=1}^{N}
and

\{\widehat{v}^{(n)}_{j}\}_{n=1}^{N}
, and equations (
6.38
), (
6.40
) define
the resulting ensemble means
(m_{j},\widehat{m}_{j})
and covariances
(C_{j},\widehat{C}_{j})
, for
j=1,\ldots,J
. The EnKS computes the backward pass, running
j
from
J-1
down to
0
, through the iteration

(6.76a)

\displaystyle v_{J}^{(n),\text{s}}

\displaystyle=v_{J}^{(n)},\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(6.76b)

\displaystyle v_{j}^{(n),\text{s}}

\displaystyle=v_{j}^{(n)}+G_{j}(v_{j+1}^{(n),\text{s}}-\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}),\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(6.76c)

\displaystyle G_{j}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{j}\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{+},

(6.76d)

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}(v_{j}^{(n)}-m_{j})\otimes(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}-\widehat{m}_{j+1}).

Note the similarity to the RTS smoother backward pass (
6.66
).

6.4.5
Reanalysis

Reanalysis
, also known as retrospective analysis, is concerned with retrospectively producing an estimate of the trajectory of a system, or the conditional probability density function of the states given the observations. Since it is done retrospectively, it is offline, in the sense described in Remark
6.6
, making this task amenable to smoothing. However, filtering algorithms are often used to produce reanalysis for reasons of computational cost.

An important feature of reanalysis is that it consolidates possibly sparse, irregular observations to give, at every time step, a complete state estimate of the system on the model state space,
one which also incorporates information from the model dynamics. The extent to which the information from observed parts of the system can transfer to unobserved parts is formalized under the concept of
observability
; see the bibliography Section
6.8
for references.

Reanalysis datasets, due to the impact of the observations, are often assumed to
exhibit lower effects from model error (see Section
6.5
), in contrast
to the raw forecasts produced by the model. This is discussed in Subsection
8.2.3
.
Both because the output of reanalysis is produced on a regular grid, and because it is thought to
exhibit lower model error, reanalyses are often used to train machine learning forecast models such as those described in Chapter
14
.

6.5
Model Error

We now consider the data assimilation problem in the setting where parts of the model are unknown, building on discussions
in Section
1.5
for the general inverse problem. To describe
forecast model error
, we introduce parameter

\vartheta
that captures unknown aspects of the systematic part of the dynamical model.
The stochastic dynamics model becomes

(6.77a)

\displaystyle v^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\vartheta}(v^{\dagger}_{j})+\xi^{\dagger}_{j},\>j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},

(6.77b)

\displaystyle v^{\dagger}_{0}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}\bigl(m_{0},C_{0}\bigr),\>\xi^{\dagger}_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}\bigl(0,\Sigma\bigr)\>\text{i.i.d.}

To describe
observation model error
we introduce parameter

\varphi
which captures unknown aspects of the observation
operator.
The data model becomes

(6.78a)

\displaystyle y^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=h_{\varphi}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1})+\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1},\>j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},

(6.78b)

\displaystyle\eta^{\dagger}_{j}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}\bigl(0,\Gamma\bigr)\>\text{i.i.d.}

In the preceding we again invoke Assumption
6.1
.

Remark 6.33
.

In the following we work with unknown parameter
\theta=(\vartheta,\Sigma,\varphi,\Gamma)
,
or choose
\theta
to be a subset of these four parameters, as we recognize that
this will often be natural in applications. Furthermore, whilst we have general parameterization of the vector fields, the covariance matrices
\Sigma
and

\Gamma
may themselves be parameterized; for example we might write a covariance matrix as an
unknown scalar non-negative real parameter multiplying the identity, or we might
parameterize it in terms of a Cholesky factor.
∎

In the next two subsections we formulate
model error in the filtering and smoothing contexts.
Chapter
8
is devoted to detailed algorithms
concerned with learning model error.

6.5.1
Model Error: Filtering

It is possible to learn about model error using filtering. To illustrate
this idea we consider the setting in which
\Sigma
and
\Gamma
are known and the unknown
\theta
comprises only

\vartheta
and
\varphi
appearing in
\Psi_{\vartheta}
and
h_{\varphi}
, respectively. Consider the
following dynamical system, holding for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}:

(6.79a)

\displaystyle v^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\vartheta_{j}^{\dagger}}(v^{\dagger}_{j})+\xi^{\dagger}_{j},

(6.79b)

\displaystyle\vartheta^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\vartheta^{\dagger}_{j},

(6.79c)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{j}^{\dagger},

(6.79d)

\displaystyle y^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=h_{\varphi_{j}^{\dagger}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1})+\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}.

This can now be viewed as a filtering problem for

\{(v^{\dagger}_{j},\vartheta^{\dagger}_{j},\varphi^{\dagger}_{j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
given the data
generated by
\{y^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}.

Remark 6.34
.

As it stands it is slightly out of the scope
of problems studied in Section
6.3
, because no noise
appears in the evolution of the unknown parameters. However, as discussed in more detail in
Section
6.7
, filtering can be extended to this setting. Alternatively
it is possible to replace equations (
6.79
b,
6.79
c)
by stochastic processes and to use an average over index
j
of the filtering solution to provide a parameter estimate; in this context linear autoregressive models, as developed in Section
14.4
, are natural replacements
for (
6.79
b,
6.79
c).
∎

6.5.2
Model Error: Smoothing

It is also possible to consider model error in the context of smoothing.
To formulate learning of model error in the context of a smoothing problem we put a prior on
(V^{\dagger},\theta)
, defined by the stochastic dynamics model (
6.77
)
for
V^{\dagger}|\theta
, and a prior on
\theta
; we then
condition on a likelihood defined by the data model. As in the preceding subsection, we consider the setting in which
\Sigma
and
\Gamma
are known and the unknown
\theta
comprises only

\vartheta
and
\varphi
appearing in
\Psi_{\vartheta}
and
h_{\varphi}
.
Building on Section
6.4
we define prior

(6.80)

\rho(V^{\dagger},\theta)\propto\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2}|v^{\dagger}_{0}-m_{0}|_{C_{0}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}|v^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\Psi_{\vartheta}\bigl(v^{\dagger}_{j}\bigr)|_{\Sigma}^{2}\Bigr)\rho_{\theta}(\theta),

where we have written the prior on
V^{\dagger}|\theta

and then multiplied by prior

\rho_{\theta}(\theta)
on
\theta.

As in Section
6.4
we define

\eta^{\dagger}:=\{\eta^{\dagger}_{1},\ldots,\eta^{\dagger}_{J}\},

noting that
\eta^{\dagger}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathsf{\Gamma})
, where
\mathsf{\Gamma}
is block diagonal with
\Gamma
in
each diagonal block. If we then define

\mathsf{h}_{\varphi}(V^{\dagger}):=\bigl\{h_{\varphi}(v^{\dagger}_{1}),\ldots,h_{\varphi}(v^{\dagger}_{J})\bigr\},

then the data model may be written as

Y^{\dagger}=\mathsf{h}_{\varphi}(V^{\dagger})+\eta^{\dagger}.

We are interested in finding
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(V^{\dagger},\theta|Y^{\dagger})=\Pi^{Y^{\dagger}}(V^{\dagger},\theta).
We may apply Bayes Theorem
1.3

with prior
\rho
, noting that, under this prior,
V^{\dagger}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp\eta^{\dagger},
the standard setting for Bayesian inversion
from Chapter
1
. We obtain

(6.81)

\Pi^{Y^{\dagger}}(V^{\dagger},\theta)\propto\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2}|Y^{\dagger}-\mathsf{h}_{\varphi}(V^{\dagger})|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2}\Bigr)\rho(V^{\dagger},\theta).

6.6
Surrogate Modeling

As for inverse problems, one of the earliest, and still one of the most prevalent,
uses of machine learning in the field of data assimilation is
surrogate modeling.
This section, then, introduces the use of machine
learning in data assimilation, a topic we expand upon in the next four chapters.

We are interested in applications where evaluating
\Psi
is computationally expensive, but where we have a surrogate model
\Psi^{\prime}
that approximates
\Psi
and can be cheaply evaluated. We would like to use such a surrogate model to accelerate data assimilation, similar to the use of surrogate models in the general inverse problem setting in Section
1.6
. To obtain such a surrogate model we make the following assumption about available data:

Data Assumption 6.35
.

Assume that we have
V^{\dagger}=\{v^{\dagger}_{n}\}_{n=0}^{N}
generated from the dynamics model (
6.1
).

We use index
n
within the data assumption, for consistency with the chapters on inverse
problems, and with Chapter
14
on time-series forecasting.
Imagine a situation where
\Psi
is not known, but a model of the form (
6.1
)
is assumed to hold, for known
\Sigma.
Given the preceding Data Assumption
6.35
,
we can then train
\Psi^{\prime}
as a supervised learning problem on pairs
\{(v^{\dagger}_{n},v^{\dagger}_{n+1})\}_{n=0}^{N-1}
