using variants of the time-series forecasting methods discussed in Chapter
14
.
The resulting trained model
\Psi^{\prime}
will then approximate
\Psi.

Remark 6.36
.

Data Assumption
6.35
may hold, for example, if
\Psi
represents output of a differential
equation solve over a fixed time interval;
\Psi^{\prime}
will then represent a neural network surrogate,
which may be cheaper to evaluate. However, in many situations Data Assumption
6.35
may not be
reasonable: we only have access to a trajectory through data assimilation.
For example it may be advantageous to take
V^{\dagger}
to be data obtained by reanalysis; see Subsection
6.4.5
. Another alternative is to train a surrogate model using observations
Y^{\dagger}
, employing the maximum likelihood methods described in Chapter
8
.
∎

It is clearly of importance to understand how the use of
\Psi^{\prime}
, in place of
\Psi
, affects the
filtering or smoothing distributions. This is the subject of the next two subsections.

6.6.1
Filtering with Surrogate Dynamics

Consider the filtering problem of estimating signal

\{v^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j\geq 0}
given observations
\{y^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
in the setting where the dynamics–observation models (
6.1
), (
6.2
) reduce to

(6.82a)

\displaystyle v^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v^{\dagger}_{j}),

(6.82b)

\displaystyle y^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=Hv^{\dagger}_{j+1}+\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1};

that is, we assume the dynamics are noiseless (
\Sigma=0
) and that the observation map
h(\cdot)=H\cdot
is linear.
We make the same Assumption
6.1
, on the noise and initial condition, as detailed at the start of this chapter. Consider the 3DVar method (
6.23
), with
s=0
,
applied in the setting (
6.82
); we obtain

v_{j+1}=(I-KH)\Psi(v_{j})+Ky^{\dagger}_{j+1}.

The following theorem proves long-time accuracy for a 3DVar filtering algorithm that uses the surrogate dynamics
\Psi^{\prime}
rather than the true dynamics
\Psi
, provided the surrogate model is sufficiently accurate and the observations sufficiently informative. Thus
we consider deploying the algorithm

(6.83)

v_{j+1}=(I-KH)\Psi^{\prime}(v_{j})+Ky^{\dagger}_{j+1}.

Theorem 6.37
.

Consider the 3DVar method (
6.83
), using approximate forecast model
\Psi^{\prime},

and driven by observations
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
from (
6.82
).
Assume that
\Psi^{\prime}
is close to
\Psi
in the sense that, for some

\delta\in[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\sup_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigl|(I-KH)\bigl(\Psi(v)-\Psi^{\prime}(v)\bigr)\bigr|

\displaystyle=\delta.

Assume, further, the observability condition that there exists constant
\lambda\in(0,1)
so that

(6.84)

\displaystyle\sup_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|(I-KH)D\Psi(v)|

\displaystyle\leq\lambda.

Denote
\epsilon:=\mathbb{E}|K\eta^{\dagger}_{j}|.

Then, the 3DVar estimate
v_{j}
based on (
6.83
) satisfies

\limsup_{j\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}|v_{j}-v^{\dagger}_{j}|\leq\frac{\epsilon+\delta}{1-\lambda},

where
v^{\dagger}_{j}
is given by (
6.82
).

Proof.

The 3DVar update with the approximate forecast model from (
6.83
)
can be written as

v_{j+1}=(I-KH)(\Psi(v_{j})+b_{j})+Ky^{\dagger}_{j+1},

where
b_{j}:=\Psi^{\prime}(v_{j})-\Psi(v_{j})

represents bias in the forecast model at time
j
.
From (
6.82
) we obtain

y^{\dagger}_{j+1}=H\Psi(v^{\dagger}_{j})+\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}

so that

\displaystyle v_{j+1}-v^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=(I-KH)\bigl(\Psi(v_{j})+b_{j}\bigr)+K\bigl(H\Psi(v^{\dagger}_{j})+\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}\bigr)-\Psi(v^{\dagger}_{j})

\displaystyle=(I-KH)(\Psi(v_{j})-\Psi(v^{\dagger}_{j}))+K\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}+(I-KH)b_{j}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}(I-KH)D\Psi(sv_{j}+(1-s)v^{\dagger}_{j})(v_{j}-v^{\dagger}_{j})\,ds+K\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}+(I-KH)b_{j},

where in the last line we used the mean value theorem for
\Psi
on the line segment
sv_{j}+(1-s)v^{\dagger}_{j}
for
s\in[0,1]
. By the triangle inequality, the error is bounded by

\displaystyle|v_{j+1}-v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl(\int_{0}^{1}|(I-KH)D\Psi(sv_{j}+(1-s)v^{\dagger}_{j})|\,ds\Bigr)|v_{j}-v^{\dagger}_{j}|+|K\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}|+\delta

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl(\int_{0}^{1}\lambda\,ds\Bigr)|v_{j}-v^{\dagger}_{j}|+|K\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}|+\delta.

Taking an expectation over the measurement errors, we have

\mathbb{E}|v_{j+1}-v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|\leq\lambda\mathbb{E}|v_{j}-v_{j}^{\dagger}|+\epsilon+\delta.

Letting
e_{j}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}|v_{j}-v^{\dagger}_{j}|
and applying the discrete Gronwall inequality we have

e_{j}\leq\lambda^{j}e_{0}+(\epsilon+\delta)\frac{1-\lambda^{j}}{1-\lambda}.

Noting that
\lambda\in(0,1)
it follows that
\lambda^{j}\rightarrow 0
as
j\rightarrow\infty
and so the result follows.
∎

Remark 6.38
.

Theorem
6.37
highlights that running the 3DVar algorithm for long times can prevent small model error from accumulating. Moreover, this model error only needs to be controlled in the unobserved directions. That is, the overall error of the recovered state will be small if
(I-KH)b_{j}
is small for all
j
, rather than the overall model error
b_{j}
being small. Ideally, the gain
K
in 3DVar is chosen such that the conditions in the assumption of Theorem
6.37
hold. See the bibliography Section
6.8
for one such guarantee under an observability condition.
∎

Remark 6.39
.

As discussed in the bibliography Section
6.8
, similar accuracy results can be established for other filtering algorithms. These results rely on
observability conditions on the true dynamics and observation model
(\Psi,H)
and on accuracy of the surrogate model
\Psi^{\prime}
in the unobserved part of the state-space. Notice that in Theorem
6.37
we do not assume long-time accuracy or stability of the surrogate dynamics defined by
\Psi^{\prime}
, or of the true dynamics defined by
\Psi
, but we can nevertheless obtain long-time accuracy of filtering estimates by leveraging the
stability properties of the map
(I-KH)\Psi
; this is a form of observability.
∎

6.6.2
Smoothing with Surrogate Dynamics

Recall definitions of
V^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger}
, and
Y^{\dagger}_{j}
from (
6.3
).
From (
6.11
) we obtain the prior on
V^{\dagger}
from the dynamics model,
a probability density function in
\mathbb{R}^{d(J+1)}
given by

(6.85)

\rho(V^{\dagger})\propto\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2}|v^{\dagger}_{0}-m_{0}|_{C_{0}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}|v^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\Psi\bigl(v^{\dagger}_{j}\bigr)|_{\Sigma}^{2}\Bigr).

Immediately after (
6.11
) we define

\displaystyle\eta^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\{\eta^{\dagger}_{1},\ldots,\eta^{\dagger}_{J}\}\in\mathbb{R}^{kJ},

\displaystyle\mathsf{h}(V^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=\bigl\{h(v^{\dagger}_{1}),\ldots,h(v^{\dagger}_{J})\bigr\},

where
\eta^{\dagger}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathsf{\Gamma})
, and
\mathsf{\Gamma}
is block diagonal with
\Gamma
in
each diagonal block. We then obtain the posterior, an element of
\mathcal{P}\bigl(\mathbb{R}^{d(J+1)}\bigr)
,

(6.86)

\Pi(V^{\dagger})\propto\mathsf{l}(Y^{\dagger}|V^{\dagger})\rho(V^{\dagger}),

where

(6.87)

\mathsf{l}(Y^{\dagger}|V^{\dagger})\propto\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2}|Y^{\dagger}-\mathsf{h}(V^{\dagger})|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2}\Bigr).

(We drop the superscript
Y^{\dagger}
on the posterior as it is not central to
the following discussion.)
If we use a surrogate model
\Psi^{\prime}
to accelerate computations we will change the
prior dynamics model to have the form

(6.88)

\rho^{\prime}(V^{\dagger})\propto\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2}|v^{\dagger}_{0}-m_{0}|_{C_{0}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}|v^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\Psi^{\prime}\bigl(v^{\dagger}_{j}\bigr)|_{\Sigma}^{2}\Bigr).

The resulting posterior is

(6.89)

\Pi^{\prime}(V^{\dagger})\propto\mathsf{l}(Y^{\dagger}|V^{\dagger})\rho^{\prime}(V^{\dagger}).

We are interested in what effect the use of a surrogate model has on the posterior.
As a first step we simply assume that
\rho^{\prime}
is close to
\rho
and ask what can be said about the closeness of
\Pi^{\prime}
and
\Pi.
In the following theorem and proof, all integrals are over
\mathbb{R}^{d(J+1)}
.

Theorem 6.40
.

Assume that
Z:=\int\mathsf{l}(Y^{\dagger}|V^{\dagger})\rho(V^{\dagger})\,dV^{\dagger}>0.
Then, there is a constant
C>0
such that, for all

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
sufficiently small,

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\Pi,\Pi^{\prime})\leq C\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime}).

Proof.

In this proof
Z^{\prime}:=\int\mathsf{l}(Y^{\dagger}|V^{\dagger})\rho^{\prime}(V^{\dagger})\,dV^{\dagger}>0
and
\mathsf{l}(V^{\dagger})=\mathsf{l}(Y^{\dagger}|V^{\dagger}).

We have

\displaystyle 2\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\Pi,\Pi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int\Bigl|\frac{1}{Z}\mathsf{l}(V^{\dagger})\rho(V^{\dagger})-\frac{1}{Z^{\prime}}\mathsf{l}(V^{\dagger})\rho^{\prime}(V^{\dagger})\Bigr|\,dV^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\leq I_{1}+I_{2},

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle:=\int\Bigl|\frac{1}{Z}\mathsf{l}(V^{\dagger})\rho(V^{\dagger})-\frac{1}{Z}\mathsf{l}(V^{\dagger})\rho^{\prime}(V^{\dagger})\Bigr|\,dV^{\dagger},

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle:=\int\Bigl|\frac{1}{Z}\mathsf{l}(V^{\dagger})\rho^{\prime}(V^{\dagger})-\frac{1}{Z^{\prime}}\mathsf{l}(V^{\dagger})\rho^{\prime}(V^{\dagger})\Bigr|\,dV^{\dagger}.

Since
Z>0
and
\mathsf{l}(V^{\dagger})
is uniformly bounded with respect to
V^{\dagger}
we deduce that, for some
C_{1}>0,

I_{1}\leq C_{1}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime}).

It also follows that, for some
C_{2}>0,

|Z-Z^{\prime}|\leq\int\mathsf{l}(V^{\dagger})|\rho(V^{\dagger})-\rho^{\prime}(V^{\dagger})|\,dV^{\dagger}\leq C_{2}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime}).

Hence, for all
\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
sufficiently small,
Z^{\prime}>\frac{1}{2}Z>0.
Finally
we deduce that, for
C_{3}=Z^{\prime}>0
,

I_{2}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\frac{1}{Z}-\frac{1}{Z^{\prime}}}}\right|Z\leq C_{3}|Z-Z^{\prime}|.

The result follows.
∎

Remark 6.41
.

The sense in which
\rho^{\prime}
is close to
\rho
depends on details of surrogate models that we do not get into in these notes. In particular the approximation
theorems we allude to in Section
12.5
are valid, in their simplest form,
over compact sets
D
; in contrast, the prior distribution here is supported on the whole Euclidean space. However, by making assumptions about the behaviour of
\Psi
and
\Psi^{\prime}
at infinity, which controls errors outside
D
, and by choosing
D
large enough, it is possible to deduce that
\rho^{\prime}
is close to
\rho
.
∎

6.7
Generalizations

Here we conclude with some remarks on generalizations
of the setting we adopt in this chapter. First we observe that the stochastic
dynamics model (
6.1
) and data model (
6.2
) are readily generalized
to settings in which the maps
\Psi(\cdot)
and
h(\cdot),
as well as the covariances

\Sigma
and
\Gamma
, are dependent on the time-index
j.
It is also possible
to allow degenerate noises (positive semidefinite covariances),
and the case where no noise is present in the
dynamics (
\Sigma\equiv 0
) arises frequently. Furthermore, all the
algorithms in this chapter, except for the Kalman filter which leverages Gaussian structure,
admit generalizations to settings in which the noises

\{\xi^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}},\{\eta^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
are independent i.i.d. centered but
non-Gaussian
sequences.
Adding
correlation
to the noises is also possible.
For example it is possible to consider correlation across the discrete time index
j
.
This significantly complicates filtering, but it is possible to accommodate it if
the noise itself is generated by a Markov process.
Adding correlation to the noises across the discrete time index
j

also complicates smoothing, but can also be handled, typically at additional
computational cost. In addition, allowing for correlation between
the dynamics and observational noise is also possible. Finally we note
that assuming differentiability, or even continuity, of the maps
\Psi(\cdot)
and

h(\cdot)
is also not necessary; however
assuming differentiability of
\Psi(\cdot)
and
h(\cdot)
facilitates specific
filtering methods such as the ExKF and gradient-based
algorithms for smoothing such as 4DVar.

Bibliography

For overviews of the subjects of data assimilation and filtering/smoothing, see the
textbooks
[
262
,
305
,
409
,
32

,

]
and the review paper
[

]
. Furthermore the
books
[
270
,
327
]
and the review paper
[
97
]

comprise pedagogical introductions to data assimilation in the context of weather forecasting, turbulence modeling, and geophysical sciences, respectively. The importance of data assimilation for numerical weather forecasting was articulated in
[
372
]
, then known as
objective analysis
, and connected to sequential estimation theory in
[
197
]
. The terminology of
prediction
and
analysis
to define
filtering was introduced in the weather forecasting community
[
372
]
.

The Kalman filter was introduced in
[
267
]
.
Discussion of the steady state covariance, and results concerning
convergence to the steady state, may be found in
[
298
]
.
The ideas of 3DVar for the solution of the analysis step were introduced in
the context of weather forecasting in
[
323
]
.
Using the formula (
6.26
) directly, rather than carrying out the minimization (
6.25
) numerically, is known as
optimal interpolation
in the weather forecasting literature. Optimal interpolation was proposed earlier by Eliassen (1954) and Gandin (1963); see
[
58
]
for bibliographical notes.
For definition of the (cycled) 3DVar
algorithm as employed here, see
[
305
]
.
For analysis of the (cycled) 3DVar algorithm see
[
304
,
349
]
.
The ExKF

is described and analyzed in
[
262
]
and its use in numerical weather prediction is considered in
[
197
]
. For theoretical results concerning Gaussian approximations for evolving probability distributions with small variance, see
[
421
]
.

The EnKF is introduced in
[
162
]
; see

[
32
,
161
,
305
,
409
]

for more recent discussion.
The methodology based around optimization has a probabilistic interpretation
in the Gaussian setting, and this can be used to justify the empirical
approximation (
6.34
) (and also Remark
6.15
); in the Gaussian setting see

[
307
,
329
,
291
]
and
in the near-Gaussian setting see
[
102
,
92
]
. The paper
[
427
]
establishes long-time accuracy of EnKF, as a state estimator, for a wide class of partially observed chaotic dynamical systems; moreover,
[
427
]
also shows that long-time accuracy still holds when the true dynamics are replaced with a sufficiently accurate surrogate model.

The impact of sampling error on EnKFs in the case that the signal is a Gaussian process is addressed in
[
417
]
. Localization and inflation are reviewed in
[
161
]
. Insights into localization are given in
[
183
,
10
,
65
,
9
,
12
,
11
,
488
]
. Localization in the context of using the EnKF to solve inverse problems
is discussed in
[
1
]
. For further background on the derivation of (
6.44
), see
[

, Page 158]
and also
[

,
10
]
.

There are many variants of the EnKF, including ensemble square-root filters that are more computationally suited for high-dimensional problems than the formulation presented in this chapter
[
473
,
254
]
. Since these variants avoid the construction of the covariance matrices, localization is often implemented in these filters through
domain localization
, where the spatial domain is divided into multiple areas, and analyses are done locally in each. A popular EnKF variant that uses domain localization is the local ensemble transform Kalman filter (LETKF)
[
254
]
.

We note that besides the considerations about spurious correlations and forecast covariance rank discussed in the chapter, localization can also be interpreted in a dynamical systems context. For an EnKF, one generally needs enough ensemble members to span the unstable–neutral subspace, corresponding to the number of non-negative Lyapunov exponents, in order to prevent filter divergence. This observation is supported by the fact that, in the case of linear dynamics, the forecast covariance matrix will collapse onto the unstable–neutral subspace; see the early work of
[
475
]
and the review of data assimilation for chaotic dynamics
[
98
]
for references to these results. It has been observed, however, that dynamical systems such as the atmosphere are locally low dimensional
[
379
,
365
]
; that is, the dynamics within a spatial region may have a significantly lower dimension (as quantified by the dimension of the subspace spanned by the fastest growing modes) than that of the entire system, implying that filtering may be successful with a small ensemble when localization is applied.

For an introduction to the particle filter see
[
148
]
.
For the OPF see
[
149
]
. Details of the derivation of the OPF, using the same
notation that we have here, and in particular when the observation operator is linear, may be found in
[
424
]
.
Particle filters typically suffer from weight collapse in high-dimensional problems:
all weights become zero, except one
[
66
,
443
]
.

The Rauch–Tung–Striebel (RTS) smoother was introduced in
[
405
]
. For a discussion of 4DVar, in the context of weather forecasting, see

[
176
]
. As with all MAP estimators,
the result of applying 4DVar is simply a point
estimator. To obtain information about the posterior with density proportional
to
\exp\bigl(-{\mathsf{J}}(V)\bigr)
requires the use of methods such as MCMC
[
87
]
, SMC
[
143
]
or variational
methods
[
264
]
. Hybrid methods that combine 4DVar with ensembles are also popular ways to estimate uncertainty for 4DVar
[
97
]
.

The ensemble Kalman smoother (EnKS) was introduced in
[
163
]
. The form of the EnKS that we discuss here is from
[
394
]
; this work also discusses the relationship between the EnKS and the RTS smoother. See
[
400
]
for a discussion of the EnKS and the ensemble RTS smoother in the more general framework of ensemble transport smoothers.

The concept of reanalysis originates in atmospheric science
[
481
]
. Although operational weather forecasting has been done for decades, there have been considerable changes in forecast models and data assimilation methods, necessitating reanalysis to have a consistent trajectory estimate over decadal timescales.
One of the first reanalysis datasets for the atmosphere was produced by the National Centers for Environmental Prediction (NCEP) and the National Center for Atmospheric Research (NCAR)
[
268
]
.
The subject of observability is reviewed in
[
337
]
.

The spread–error relationship for ensemble forecasts is discussed in
[
177
]
. Rank histograms, also called Talagrand diagrams, were introduced in the context of ensemble forecasts by several independent groups; see
[
466
,
222
]
. Theorem
6.29
was stated in
[
36
]
.
Model error in data assimilation is often modeled by Gaussian noise, and encapsulated in the model noises
\xi^{\dagger}
. Deterministic formulations of model error are reviewed in
[
100
]
. The special case of model bias is considered in
[
142
]
. Time-correlated model error is considered in
[
19
]
. Methods for correcting model error are discussed in Chapter
8
, and a bibliography provided there.
While the effect of model bias on data assimilation is an open research topic,
[
142
]
analyzed Kalman filtering in the presence of model bias. Under observability conditions,
[
337
]
showed that the Kalman
gain
K
can be designed to satisfy the condition in (
6.84
) for stability of a data assimilation scheme with linear dynamics;
this is also related to the design of Luenberger observers in control theory.

The study of surrogate modeling, for both filtering and
smoothing, is an area where machine learning has a simple and direct
potential for impact on data assimilation, and is an area where substantial
initial and continuing activity at the data assimilation and machine learning
interface is taking place.
Machine learning models for numerical weather forecasting have recently received significant attention; see, e.g.,
[

,

,

,

Data assimilation using learned forecast models has been considered in

3
,

6.37
was also proved in
[
3
]
. Continuous data assimilation with surrogate models, and analysis of the amount of training data needed for accurate nudging, is explored in
[
315
]
. The importance of these forecast models correctly reproducing the Lyapunov spectrum and forecast error covariance was explored in
[
382
]
. Including the Lyapunov spectrum and attractor dimension into the training process was explored in
[
387
]
. Instead of learning a full forward model, learning an adjoint model for use in 4DVar was considered in
[

]
.

Hybrid methods combining a numerical and learned forecast model in data assimilation and ensemble forecasting have been considered in
[

,
40
,
109
]
. The latter used a large ensemble of a learned forecast model, along with a smaller ensemble of a more expensive numerical model solving the equations of motion, in an EnKF, mitigating the need for localization.
Combining a small high-fidelity ensemble with a large ensemble of reduced-order models in an EnKF using control variates was considered in
[

,

]
. A multilevel EnKF was introduced in

Chapter 7
Variational Inference for Data Assimilation

In this chapter we use the variational formulation of Bayes Theorem, introduced in
Chapter
2
, to frame data assimilation problems
as optimization problems over probability measures; both smoothing
and filtering problems are considered. In Section
7.1
we introduce the variational formulation of the smoothing problem, in analogy to the procedures in Chapter
2
for inverse problems; Section
7.2
extends the variational formulation to filtering.
In both of these first two sections we also introduce algorithmic frameworks, stemming from the variational formulations.
In Section
7.3
we show how filtering may be linked to smoothing by imposing a filtering structure on a variational formulation of smoothing. Sections
7.4
and
7.5
introduce examples of variational algorithms, based on the formulations in Sections
7.1
and

7.2
respectively. We conclude the chapter with bibliographic remarks in Section
7.6
.

Remark 7.1
.

We are focused on the problem of finding certain distributions on
the state of the stochastic dynamical system (
6.1
) conditioned on
data from (
6.2
). Throughout we employ the notational conventions
established in Subsection
6.1.1
, in particular those displayed in (
6.3
)
and the definition of
Y^{\dagger}_{0}
as the empty set
\varnothing.

We write
\mathcal{P}:=\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for the space of probability
measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
; then, the probability distribution of the state of the system at any fixed time belongs to
\mathcal{P}.

It is also convenient to denote by
\mathcal{P}^{j}:=\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d(j+1)})
the space of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d(j+1)}
; then, the probability distribution of
V^{\dagger}_{j}
belongs to
\mathcal{P}^{j},
and, in particular, the distribution of
V^{\dagger}
belongs to
\mathcal{P}^{J}
.

Consider the smoothing problem defined in Subsection
6.2.2
; in this context we will adapt the notation from Remark
1.5
to allow definition of marginal
\kappa
on
Y^{\dagger}
determined by the joint
\gamma
on
(Y^{\dagger},V^{\dagger})
.
Recall, also, the filtering problem defined in Subsection
6.2.1
, and the notation
\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1})=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})
for the predictive distribution; here, and in other chapters, it will be useful to define
the joint distribution
\gamma_{j+1}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1},y^{\dagger}_{j+1})\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1},y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})=\mathsf{l}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v^{\dagger}_{j+1})\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1})
, on state and observation at
time
j+1
, and its marginal
\kappa_{j+1}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1})=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})
on the observation coordinate.
∎

Throughout the chapter we make the following assumption
about data available to define the filtering and smoothing problems variationally,
and for learning the parameters of models for filtering and smoothing through these variational
formulations.

Data Assumption 7.2
.

The available data consists of
Y^{\dagger}:=\{y^{\dagger}_{1},\ldots,y^{\dagger}_{J}\}
, a single draw from the marginal
\kappa

on the observed data.

7.1
Variational Formulation of Smoothing

From (
6.9
), the density for the smoothing distribution
\mathbb{P}(V^{\dagger}|Y^{\dagger})
is given by

\displaystyle\Pi^{Y^{\dagger}}(V^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\propto\mathsf{l}(Y^{\dagger}|V^{\dagger})\rho(V^{\dagger}).

Detailed formulae for the prior
\rho
and likelihood
\mathsf{l}
are given in Subsection
6.2.2
.
The following theorem shows that the smoothing density arises as the solution of a variational problem.
The following result is a direct application of the variational
formulation of Bayes Theorem
in Theorem
2.1
to the smoothing problem
defined in (
6.9
).

Theorem 7.3
.

Consider the likelihood
\mathsf{l}(Y^{\dagger}|V^{\dagger})
and prior
\rho(V^{\dagger})
given in (
6.10
), and let

(7.1a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(q)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|\rho)-\mathbb{E}^{q}\bigl[\log\mathsf{l}(Y^{\dagger}|\cdot)\bigr],

(7.1b)

\displaystyle q_{\text{\sc{opt}}}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{q\in\mathcal{P}^{J}}{\mathsf{J}}(q).

Then, the minimizer of
{\mathsf{J}}
over
\mathcal{P}^{J}
is the smoothing density:
q_{\text{\sc{opt}}}=\Pi^{Y^{\dagger}}.

This formulation of the smoothing problem can be used as the basis for algorithms to learn approximations for the smoothing distribution from data: we replace
\mathcal{P}^{J}
in (
7.1
)
by some
\mathcal{Q}^{J}\subset\mathcal{P}^{J}
and solve

(7.2a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(q)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|\rho)-\mathbb{E}^{q}\bigl[\log\mathsf{l}(Y^{\dagger}|\cdot)\bigr],

(7.2b)

\displaystyle q^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{q\in\mathcal{Q}^{J}}{\mathsf{J}}(q).

In Section
7.4
we give an explicit example of a choice of
\mathcal{Q}^{J}

that leads to actionable algorithms.

Remark 7.4
.

In this section we have focused on
the idea of solving the smoothing problem for a given realization

Y^{\dagger}
– we work under Data Assumption
7.2
.
However, it is of interest to learn the dependence of the solution on the data instance
Y^{\dagger}.

This is known as
amortization
and is typically tackled by considering learning problems
defined over multiple realizations of the data
Y^{\dagger}
; see Subsection
10.2.2
for the amortized version of the methodology introduced here.
∎

7.2
Variational Formulation of Filtering

Recall the prediction (
{\mathsf{P}}
) and analysis (
{\mathsf{A}}_{j}:={\mathsf{A}}(\cdot\,;y^{\dagger}_{j+1})
) maps
that define the evolution of the filtering distribution in (
6.4
), (
6.5
). The mapping from the filter at time
j
,
\pi_{j}
, to the filter at time
j+1
,
\pi_{j+1}
, is comprised of two steps. The forecast step

\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}={\mathsf{P}}\pi_{j}
is defined by (
6.6
), and the analysis step, as detailed in (
6.8
), is an application of Bayes Theorem
1.3
with likelihood
\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v^{\dagger}_{j+1})
and prior
\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}
. The analysis density
\pi_{j+1}
can be written as the solution to an optimization problem using the variational formulation of Bayes Theorem
2.1
:

(7.3a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(q)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|\widehat{\pi}_{j+1})-\mathbb{E}^{q}\bigl[\log\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|\cdot)\bigr],

(7.3b)

\displaystyle\pi_{j+1}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{q\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(q).

The preceding formulation assumes that we have access to the true prior
\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}
. We now consider densities
q^{\prime}_{j}
recursively defined by the prediction–analysis cycle

(7.4a)

\displaystyle q^{\prime}_{0}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0},

(7.4b)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(q)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|{\mathsf{P}}q^{\prime}_{j})-\mathbb{E}^{q}\bigl[\log\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|\cdot)\bigr],

(7.4c)

\displaystyle q^{\prime}_{j+1}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{q\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(q).

Lemma 7.5
.

Applying the iteration (
7.4
) for
j=0,\ldots,J-1
yields
q^{\prime}_{j}=\pi_{j}
for
j=0,\ldots,J.

Proof.

The result holds for
j=0
by (
7.4
a). The inductive step
j\mapsto j+1
follows by
noting that, since solution of the optimization problem solves the Bayesian inverse problem that defines the analysis step

{\mathsf{A}}
defined in (
6.8
), we have

(7.5)

q_{j+1}^{\prime}={\mathsf{A}}({\mathsf{P}}q_{j}^{\prime};y^{\dagger}_{j+1})={\mathsf{A}}_{j}({\mathsf{P}}q_{j}^{\prime}),\quad q^{\prime}_{0}=\pi_{0};

this delivers the true filtering update equations (
6.4
), (
6.5
).
∎

Performing the minimization (
7.4c
) over a
restricted class
\mathcal{Q}_{j+1}
of probability densities, potentially defined
differently at each step
j
, leads to a sequence of approximate filtering densities.
To provide detail on this idea we introduce a parameterized family of candidate approximate filters
q_{j+1}(\theta)
to develop the basis of actionable algorithms, implicitly defining
\mathcal{Q}_{j+1}.
Assume that we have available to us the approximate filter at time
j
,
q^{\star}_{j}\approx\pi_{j}.
Consider a family of approximate filters at time
j+1
,
q_{j+1}(\theta),
defined via

(7.6)

q_{j+1}(\theta)={\mathsf{A}}_{\theta}({\mathsf{P}}q^{\star}_{j};y^{\dagger}_{j+1}).

Here
{\mathsf{A}}_{\theta}
is a
\theta
-parameterized family of maps, and our goal is to choose
\theta\in\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
so that
q_{j+1}(\theta)
approximates the filtering distribution
\pi_{j+1}
. Using the variational formulation of filtering in (
7.4
), with
q_{j}^{\prime}\mapsto q^{\star}_{j}
, we define the cost function (abusing notation to now view
{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}
as a function
of
\theta
)

(7.7)

{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(\theta)=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(q_{j+1}(\theta)\|{\mathsf{P}}q^{\star}_{j}\bigr)-\mathbb{E}^{{q_{j+1}(\theta)}}[\log\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|\cdot)].

We may then consider the following
optimization problem to define the best choice of
\theta
:

\theta^{\star}_{j+1}\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(\theta).

Iterating on this idea leads to the algorithm:

(7.8a)

\displaystyle q^{\star}_{0}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0},

(7.8b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}_{j+1}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(\theta),

(7.8c)

\displaystyle q^{\star}_{j+1}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{A}}_{\theta^{\star}_{j+1}}({\mathsf{P}}q^{\star}_{j};y^{\dagger}_{j+1}).

The following result establishes that the approximate filtering algorithm does indeed preserve filtering
structure.

Lemma 7.6
.

The approximate filtering algorithm defined by (
7.8
) has the
property that
q^{\star}_{j}
depends only on
Y^{\dagger}_{j}
, and not on any other components of
Y^{\dagger}.

Proof.

The result holds for
j=0
because
Y^{\dagger}_{0}=\varnothing
. Suppose that

q^{\star}_{j}
depends only on
Y^{\dagger}_{j}
. Then the objective

{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}
, and hence
\theta^{\star}_{j+1}
, depends only on
Y^{\dagger}_{j}
and

y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
. It follows from (
7.8
) that

q^{\star}_{j+1}
depends only on
Y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
. The result follows by
induction.
∎

Remark 7.7
.

In Section
7.1
we showed how to learn the solution of the
smoothing problem, under Data Assumption
7.2
, using variational methods.
In Remark
7.4
we point out that this methodology does not enable learning
of dependence, on the observed data, of the solution of the smoothing problem, because
only one instance of the observed data is provided by Data Assumption
7.2
. We draw
a sharp distinction between the variational approach described here, and the
amortized
variational approach to smoothing that is considered
(along with other amortized methodologies) in Chapter
10
: Subsection
10.2.2

demonstrates how, when multiple realizations of the observation sequence are available,
we may learn dependence of the solution to the smoothing problem on that observation sequence.

The situation for
filtering
is more complicated. In this chapter we work, as for
smoothing, under Data Assumption
7.2
. But in terms of filtering we can view this as providing

multiple
data instances, one at each filtering step
j\in\{1,\ldots,J\}.

Recall that each update of the filtering distribution,
as defined in (
6.4
), depends, through the analysis step
{\mathsf{A}}_{j}
, on an observation
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
.
This is also true of the approximate filter (
7.8
). Indeed, the proposed form of models
that we learn for filtering do learn
explicit dependence on the observations
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
used to approximate each analysis step
{\mathsf{A}}_{j}
,
as is seen in (
7.8
). We are thus using a form of amortization over time index
j
,
even within this chapter, and under the same Data Assumption
7.2
used for smoothing.

It is arguable, however, that the parameter choices
\theta^{\star}_{j}
are overfit to the specific data
Y^{\dagger}_{j}
,
and especially to
y^{\dagger}_{j}
, in the proposed methodology. One
ad hoc
approach to ameliorate this
overfitting is to average the observed
\theta^{\star}_{j}
:

(7.9)

\theta^{\star}=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\theta^{\star}_{j}

When the algorithm, coupled to the observation data
generation process, is ergodic (see Subsection
9.2.5
for discussion of ergodicity for filtering
algorithms) then this will lead, for large
J
, to a single parameter estimate for

\theta
which can, in principle, be used to define a fixed approximation to
{\mathsf{A}}:

(7.10)

q_{j+1}={\mathsf{A}}_{\theta^{\star}}({\mathsf{P}}q_{j};y^{\dagger}_{j+1}),\quad q_{0}=\pi_{0}.

This approximate filter may then be used on new data sequences

Y^{\dagger}
, different from the specific sequence appearing in Data Assumption
7.2

and used to determine
\theta^{\star}.
The algorithm retains the desired filtering structure.

Although averaging
\theta^{\star}_{j}
is a plausible approach to finding a single parameter in
{\mathsf{A}}_{\theta}(\cdot\,;y^{\dagger})
,
the selected value of
\theta^{\star}
is not derived directly from a single optimization principle.
A preferable approach is to find a single
\theta
by solving the following averaged minimization problem

(7.11a)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(\theta),

(7.11b)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(q_{j+1}(\theta)\|{\mathsf{P}}q_{j}(\theta)\bigr)-\mathbb{E}^{{q_{j+1}(\theta)}}[\log\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|\cdot)],

(7.11c)

\displaystyle q_{j+1}(\theta)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{A}}_{\theta}({\mathsf{P}}q_{j}(\theta);y^{\dagger}_{j+1}),\quad q_{0}=\pi_{0}.

Note that for such a learning algorithm parameter

\theta
does depend on the training data in a manner that breaks the filtering structure, since it sees all the training
data from Data Assumption
7.2
. However the point of this approach is to learn a method that is usable
with new data. The algorithm (
7.10
), with
\theta^{\star}
computed as in (
7.11
),
retains the filtering structure when applied to a new observation sequence
Y^{\dagger}_{j}
, different from that used in training.

In Subsection
10.2.1
we go beyond amortization for filtering through multiple
time indices, and consider use of multiple observation
sequences

Y^{\dagger}
which can
enhance learning of the dependence of each analysis step
{\mathsf{A}}(\cdot\,;y^{\dagger})
on
y^{\dagger}
.
∎

Remark 7.8
.

To be able to employ algorithms based on the objective function (
7.7
) it is necessary that
the KL divergence between
q_{j+1}(\theta)
, given by (
7.6
),
and
{\mathsf{P}}q_{j}(\theta)
, is well-defined and that we are able to compute it.
In Section
7.5
we give an example in which

q_{j}(\theta)
, together with a Gaussian approximation

{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{G}}q_{j}(\theta)
to its predictive distribution, are maintained in Gaussian form.
In Subsection
9.4.3
we show how the ideas may be used for ensemble methods where the KL divergence is not defined; we extract Gaussian approximations from the empirical distributions defined by the ensemble method.
∎

7.3
Imposing Filtering Structure on Smoothing

An alternative way to use variational Bayes to derive filtering algorithms is
to start from the variational formulation of smoothing and then impose a temporal structure on the resulting density, to enforce filtering; specifically to impose that the probability density function for state
v^{\dagger}_{j}
at time
j
only depends on past observations
Y^{\dagger}_{j}
, as defined in (
6.3
), and not future observations.
Any probability distribution can be factorized autoregressively in the form

q(V^{\dagger})=\mathfrak{q}_{0}(v^{\dagger}_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{J}\mathfrak{q}_{i}(v^{\dagger}_{i}|V^{\dagger}_{i-1});

furthermore, if
q(V^{\dagger})
solves the smoothing problem then

q(V^{\dagger})=\mathfrak{q}_{0}(v^{\dagger}_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{J}\mathfrak{q}_{i}(v^{\dagger}_{i}|V^{\dagger}_{i-1};Y^{\dagger}).

Thus each component
\mathfrak{q}_{i}
of the autoregressive factorization depends, in principle, on the entire sequence
of observed data,
Y^{\dagger}.
In contrast,
if
q\in\mathcal{P}^{J}
solves the filtering problem then

(7.12)

q(V^{\dagger})=\mathfrak{q}_{0}(v^{\dagger}_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{J}\mathfrak{q}_{i}(v^{\dagger}_{i}|V^{\dagger}_{i-1};Y^{\dagger}_{i}).

Now each component
\mathfrak{q}_{i}
of the autoregressive factorization depends only on the sequence
of observed data up to time
i
,
Y^{\dagger}_{i}.

In order to approximate a filtering distribution it is natural to seek minimizers of
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)

defined by (
7.1
a) which factorize in the form (
7.12
).
For later use we also define
q_{j}\in\mathcal{P}^{j}
by

(7.13)

q_{j}(V^{\dagger}_{j})=\mathfrak{q}_{0}(v^{\dagger}_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{j}\mathfrak{q}_{i}(v^{\dagger}_{i}|V^{\dagger}_{i-1};Y^{\dagger}_{i}).

It is important to note the distinction between
\mathfrak{q}_{j}
and
q_{j}
.
We let
\mathcal{C}^{J}\subset\mathcal{P}^{J}
denote probability density functions of the form (
7.12
).
The use of symbol
\mathcal{C}
is to invoke the conditional structure in terms of the dependence of
\mathfrak{q}_{i}
on only
Y^{\dagger}_{i}

The smoothing distribution is, in general, contained in
\mathcal{P}^{J}\backslash\mathcal{C}^{J}
. This is because the conditional distribution of
v^{\dagger}_{i}|V_{i-1}
will, for smoothing, depend on
Y^{\dagger}
and not just on
Y^{\dagger}_{i}.

Remark 7.9
.

Formally, we would like to minimize the objective
{\mathsf{J}}
from
(
7.1
a) over
\mathcal{C}^{J}
. Under Data
Assumption
7.2
, however, only one realization of
Y^{\dagger}
is
available. Direct optimization for this realization cannot ensure that
each fitted factor depends on the observations only through
Y^{\dagger}_{i}
:
even if
\mathfrak{q}_{i}
is written as a function of
Y^{\dagger}_{i}
, its fitted
parameters may depend on the full sequence
Y^{\dagger}
. The sequential
construction below circumvents this issue by ensuring that the
objective used to determine the factor at time
i
only sees
Y^{\dagger}_{i}
.
Alternatively, the dependence can be learned using amortization and
multiple observation sequences; see Subsection
10.2.1
.
∎

We now describe a way of circumventing the issue highlighted in
Remark
7.9
, different from the amortization approach. The resulting methodology
involves minimization of a sequence of objective functions
{\mathsf{J}}_{j}(\cdot)
, each of which only sees
Y^{\dagger}_{j}.
The approach is based on the following theorem, which employs the notation in (
6.10
), (
7.12
), (
7.13
) and in which, we reiterate, it is important to note the distinction between
\mathfrak{q}_{j}
and
q_{j}
:

Theorem 7.10
.

Consider
q\in\mathcal{P}^{J}
factorized in the form (
7.12
), (
7.13
)
so that
q\in\mathcal{C}^{J}
. Define the objective function
{\mathsf{J}}^{\prime}:\mathcal{C}^{J}\to\mathbb{R}
by

(7.14a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}_{j}\Bigl(\mathfrak{q}_{j}(\cdot\,|V^{\dagger}_{j-1};Y^{\dagger}_{j})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\Bigl(\mathfrak{q}_{j}(\cdot|V^{\dagger}_{j-1};Y^{\dagger}_{j})\|\mathsf{t}(\cdot,v^{\dagger}_{j-1})\Bigr)-\mathbb{E}^{\mathfrak{q}_{j}}\bigl[\log\mathsf{l}_{j}\bigr],

(7.14b)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{\prime}(q)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\mathfrak{q}_{0}\|\rho_{0})+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{E}^{V^{\dagger}_{j-1}\sim q_{j-1}}\Bigl[{\mathsf{J}}_{j}\Bigl(\mathfrak{q}_{j}(\cdot\,|V^{\dagger}_{j-1};Y^{\dagger}_{j})\Bigr)\Bigr],

where

\mathsf{l}_{j}(v^{\dagger}_{j}):=\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j}|v^{\dagger}_{j}),\qquad\mathsf{t}(v^{\dagger}_{j},v^{\dagger}_{j-1}):=\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|v^{\dagger}_{j-1}),\qquad\rho_{0}\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{0}).

Then the minimizers of
{\mathsf{J}}
given by (
7.1
a), and of
{\mathsf{J}}^{\prime}
, both over
\mathcal{C}^{J}
, agree.

Proof.

First we note that minimizing
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)
is the same as minimizing

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\cdot\|\Pi^{Y^{\dagger}})
, as shown in the proof of
Theorem
2.1
.
We will use the
\mathsf{ELBO}
introduced in Definition
15.25
and the
relationship between
\mathsf{ELBO}
, KL divergence, and likelihood established
in Theorem
15.27
.
To this end, we note that Definition
15.25
implies that

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|\Pi^{Y^{\dagger}})=\log\mathbb{P}(Y^{\dagger})-\mathsf{ELBO}\bigl(\mathbb{P}(V^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger}),q\bigr),

because
\mathbb{P}(Y^{\dagger})
is the normalization constant that turns

\mathbb{P}(V^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger})
into the conditional

\mathbb{P}(V^{\dagger}|Y^{\dagger})
.
Since the first term does not depend on
q
, minimizing

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|\Pi^{Y^{\dagger}})
over
q
is equivalent to maximizing

\mathsf{ELBO}\bigl(\mathbb{P}(V^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger}),q\bigr)=\mathbb{E}^{V^{\dagger}\sim q}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log\frac{\mathbb{P}(V^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger})}{q(V^{\dagger})}}}\right].

The joint density
\mathbb{P}(V^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger})
can be factorized
autoregressively as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(V^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=\rho_{0}(v^{\dagger}_{0})\prod_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j},y^{\dagger}_{j}|V^{\dagger}_{j-1},Y^{\dagger}_{j-1})

\displaystyle=\rho_{0}(v^{\dagger}_{0})\prod_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j-1},V^{\dagger}_{j})\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j-1},V^{\dagger}_{j-1}).

Using this, as well as the factorized structure
(
7.12
) of
q
defining membership in

\mathcal{C}^{J}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{ELBO}(\mathbb{P}(V^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger}),q)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{q}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\rho_{0}(v^{\dagger}_{0})}{\mathfrak{q}_{0}(v^{\dagger}_{0})}\prod_{j=1}^{J}\frac{\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j-1},V^{\dagger}_{j})\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j-1},V^{\dagger}_{j-1})}{\mathfrak{q}_{j}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|V^{\dagger}_{j-1};Y^{\dagger}_{j})}}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle=-\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\mathfrak{q}_{0}\|\rho_{0})+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{E}^{q}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j-1},V^{\dagger}_{j})\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j-1},V^{\dagger}_{j-1})}{\mathfrak{q}_{j}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|V^{\dagger}_{j-1};Y^{\dagger}_{j})}}}\right)}}\right].

The
j
th summand depends only on
V^{\dagger}_{j}
, so its expectation
under
q
may be replaced by expectation under
q_{j}
. Furthermore,
by (
7.13
),

q_{j}(V^{\dagger}_{j})=q_{j-1}(V^{\dagger}_{j-1})\mathfrak{q}_{j}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|V^{\dagger}_{j-1};Y^{\dagger}_{j}),

and hence this expectation may be written as an iterated expectation
under
q_{j-1}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{j}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ELBO}(\mathbb{P}(V^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger}),q)

\displaystyle=-\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\mathfrak{q}_{0}\|\rho_{0})+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{E}^{q_{j}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j-1},V^{\dagger}_{j})\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j-1},V^{\dagger}_{j-1})}{\mathfrak{q}_{j}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|V^{\dagger}_{j-1};Y^{\dagger}_{j})}}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt=-\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\mathfrak{q}_{0}\|\rho_{0})+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{E}^{q_{j-1}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathfrak{q}_{j}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j-1},V^{\dagger}_{j})\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j-1},V^{\dagger}_{j-1})}{\mathfrak{q}_{j}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|V^{\dagger}_{j-1};Y^{\dagger}_{j})}}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt=-\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\mathfrak{q}_{0}\|\rho_{0})-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{E}^{V^{\dagger}_{j-1}\sim q_{j-1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\Bigl(\mathfrak{q}_{j}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|V^{\dagger}_{j-1};Y^{\dagger}_{j})\|\mathsf{t}(v^{\dagger}_{j},v^{\dagger}_{j-1})\Bigr)-\mathbb{E}^{v^{\dagger}_{j}\sim\mathfrak{q}_{j}}\bigl[\log\mathsf{l}_{j}(v^{\dagger}_{j})\bigr]}}\right],

where the last line follows from noticing that

\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j-1},V^{\dagger}_{j})=\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j}|v^{\dagger}_{j})=\mathsf{l}_{j}(v^{\dagger}_{j})

is the observation likelihood at time
j
, and

\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j-1},V^{\dagger}_{j-1})=\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|v^{\dagger}_{j-1})=\mathsf{t}(v^{\dagger}_{j},v^{\dagger}_{j-1})
.
The final display shows that

\mathsf{ELBO}\bigl(\mathbb{P}(V^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger}),q\bigr)=-{\mathsf{J}}^{\prime}(q).

On the other hand, the definition of
{\mathsf{J}}
and the identity

\mathbb{P}(V^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger})=\mathsf{l}(Y^{\dagger}|V^{\dagger})\rho(V^{\dagger})
give

\mathsf{ELBO}\bigl(\mathbb{P}(V^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger}),q\bigr)=-{\mathsf{J}}(q).

Consequently,
{\mathsf{J}}(q)={\mathsf{J}}^{\prime}(q)
for every
q\in\mathcal{C}^{J}
, and the result
follows.
∎

Methods that exploit this representation of the variational problem for filtering are
cited in the bibliography Section
7.6
. In particular, the methods exploit the possibility of iteratively
optimizing the averaged
{\mathsf{J}}_{j}
, similarly to the methodology developed in the preceding section.

7.4
Variational Algorithm for Smoothing: Example

We may apply the ideas of Chapter
2
to the smoothing problem, working under
Data Assumption
7.2
, to develop algorithms. The purpose of this section is to
introduce an example of such an approach to smoothing, using variational inference.
To this end we recall (
7.2
) and introduce a specific choice of
\mathcal{Q}^{J}
,
the class of probability distributions within which we approximate the smoothing distribution.

Recall the weak-constraint 4DVar solution (
6.70
) which is the MAP estimator for the smoothing problem,
as introduced in Subsection
6.4.2
. Precompute this solution of an optimization problem, and call it
V^{\star}
. Now let

\mathcal{Q}^{J}=\Bigl\{q\in\mathcal{P}^{J}:q=\mathcal{N}(V^{\star},\mathsf{A}\mathsf{A}^{\top})\Big|\;\mathsf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{d(J+1)\times d(J+1)},\,\,\det(\mathsf{A})>0\Bigr\}.

Thus in solving minimization problem (
7.2
) with this
choice of variational space we seek a Gaussian approximation of the
smoothing problem, centered on a pre-computed weak-constraint 4DVar solution.
In this formulation we thus seek to optimize over parameter
\mathsf{A},

a square-root of the covariance, as in Example
4.8
; it may be natural
to impose further triangular structure on
\mathsf{A}
in order to obtain a Cholesky
factorization. An amortized version of the methodology from this section is described in Example
10.13
.

7.5
Variational Algorithm for Filtering: Example

Here we give an example of the methodology introduced in Section
7.2
,
centered around optimizing parameter
\theta
appearing in (
7.6
).
We formulate the algorithm class around the idea of learning a gain matrix,
K
, that
defines a family of updates for the approximate filtering distributions
q_{j}(\theta);

thus, in the notation of Section
7.2
,
\theta=K.

Although we will ultimately work with a Gaussian approximation of the true filtering
distribution, we start by considering a more complicated approximate model for the evolution
of the filtering distribution; we then derive the Gaussian approximation from it.

Let
v_{j}
denote a random vector whose law is intended to approximate
the filtering distribution
\pi_{j}
; in general, this law is non-Gaussian.
We assume that
v_{j}
is defined through the following 3DVar-like recursion:

(7.15a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j})+\xi_{j},\quad\xi_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)\>\text{i.i.d.},

(7.15b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{j+1}+K\bigl(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-h(\widehat{v}_{j+1})-\eta_{j+1}\bigr),\quad\eta_{j+1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Gamma)\>\text{i.i.d.},

(7.15c)

\displaystyle v_{0}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}(m_{0},C_{0}).

Now we postulate that the distribution of
v_{j}
is approximately Gaussian. We
derive the Gaussian approximation by a linearization procedure;
indeed the following Gaussian approximation
is exact if
\Psi
and
h
are linear. We seek
the filtering distribution in form
q_{j}(K)=\mathcal{N}(m_{j},C_{j})
,
and define the predictive distribution
\widehat{q}_{j+1}(K)
by the Gaussian
\mathcal{N}(\widehat{m}_{j+1},\widehat{C}_{j+1})
. A linearization procedure applied to the law of
v_{j}

evolving according to (
7.15
) then yields
the evolution
q_{j}(K)\mapsto q_{j+1}(K)
as follows:

(7.16a)

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(m_{j}),\quad\widehat{C}_{j+1}=A_{j}C_{j}A_{j}^{\top}+\Sigma,\quad A_{j}=D\Psi(m_{j})

(7.16b)

\displaystyle m_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{m}_{j+1}+K(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-h(\widehat{m}_{j+1})),

(7.16c)

\displaystyle C_{j+1}

\displaystyle=(I-KH_{j+1})\widehat{C}_{j+1}(I-KH_{j+1})^{\top}+K\Gamma K^{\top},\quad H_{j}=Dh(\widehat{m}_{j}).

The recursion resembles the extended Kalman filter from Subsection
6.3.4
, with two differences.
First, we learn the gain
K
rather than compute it
using (
6.31c
). Second, because
K
is not necessarily the
Kalman gain, we use the covariance update in
(
7.16c
); the source of this formula is
cited in the bibliography Section
7.6
.

We now generalize the optimization problems from Section
7.2

to this Gaussian approximation.
Map
{q}_{j}(K)\to\widehat{q}_{j+1}(K)
maps the Gaussian manifold into a subset of
itself and is defined by (
7.16
a)).
This mapping is not equal to
{\mathsf{P}}
, but rather is a Gaussian approximation of it,
which we denote by
{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{G}}.
Map
{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{G}}
is only defined
on the Gaussian manifold; on that manifold it has the property that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{G}}={\mathsf{P}}

if
\Psi
is linear. Map
\widehat{q}_{j+1}(K)\mapsto{q}_{j+1}(K)
also maps the Gaussian manifold into a subset of itself and is defined by (
7.16
b) and(
7.16
c); this is the map
{\mathsf{A}}_{K}(\cdot;y^{\dagger}_{j+1})
. We may generalize (
7.7
) to this
approximate Gaussian setting to obtain

{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(K)=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(q_{j+1}(K)\|{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{G}}\,q_{j}(K)\bigr)-\mathbb{E}^{q_{j+1}(K)}[\log\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|\cdot)].

Dropping an additive constant independent of
K
, the resulting
optimization objective is

(7.17)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(K)=

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\mathcal{N}(m_{j+1},C_{j+1})\|\mathcal{N}(\widehat{m}_{j+1},\widehat{C}_{j+1})\bigr)+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{N}(m_{j+1},C_{j+1})}|y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-h(\cdot)|_{\Gamma}^{2}.

Recalling the formula (
11.18
a) for the KL divergence between two
d
-dimensional Gaussians we see the first term of (
7.17
) can be evaluated in closed form.
Evaluation of the second term, for nonlinear
h
, will require quadrature or sampling.

We can then consider the online learning problem (
7.8
), in which we learn a time-dependent gain
K_{j}^{\star}
at each analysis time:

K_{j}^{\star}\in\argmin_{K\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}}{\mathsf{J}}_{j}(K);

or the offline problem (
7.11
) in which a single fixed gain
K^{\star}
is learned for all analysis times:

K^{\star}\in\argmin_{K\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}}\sum_{j=1}^{J}{\mathsf{J}}_{j}(K).

7.6
Bibliography

Several works have studied variational formulations of smoothing and filtering problems.
The use of Gaussian approximations in filtering, including via variational Bayes, is studied in the papers
[
135
,
492
,
27
]
. Combinations with ensemble methods are discussed in
[
493
]
. The paper

[
186
]
employs mean-field models.
Variational filtering is discussed in
[
332
]
, and the proof of Theorem
7.10
is given there. Using variational methods in the context of sequential Monte Carlo is
discussed in
[
357
]
. Learning parameterized filters using the variational objective introduced in Section
7.2
is considered in
[
38
]
; in particular that
work introduces and deploys the learnable Gaussian filter described in
Section
7.5
. An alternative variational objective for jointly learning a parameterized filter and the system dynamics is given in
[
81
]
. A variational formulation of the filtering and smoothing problems in continuous time, where the posterior is restricted to be in the exponential family, is provided in
[
460
]
. The variational formulation of the optimal continuous-time filter (the Kushner–Stratonovich equation) with no dynamics is derived in
[
303
]
. The latter is formally combined with a variational formulation of the Fokker–Planck equation for gradient systems in
[
294
]
, and it is shown that the solution coincides with the Kalman–Bucy filter in the linear case. The connection between 4DVar and variational inference is discussed in
[
174
]
, and the idea of using variational inference and Gaussian approximations to extend 4DVar to posterior estimation is explored in
[
172
]
.

Chapter 8
Learning the Prior for Data Assimilation

This chapter is devoted to learning priors for both smoothing and filtering problems, paralleling the material in
Chapter
3
concerning the learning of priors for inverse problems.
In the smoothing problem, the prior is determined by the stochastic dynamics model.
Thus, here, we will consider learning parameters defining the dynamics model, along with the state,
from observations. This is analogous to Section
3.1
in which we consider hierarchical modeling for
a Bayesian inverse problem:
the unknown parameter, and the parameters of the prior, are jointly learned from the single observation available.
In the filtering problem, there is a prior at each time point, found by predicting with the dynamics model,
starting from an approximation of the filtering distribution; this prior is
then combined with a likelihood to incorporate the next observation. In this chapter, we consider
learning priors for the filtering step, using Gaussian and particle-based empirical approximations.
In Section
3.3
we showed how to represent the prior as a pushforward from a latent space.
In the final part of this chapter we consider data assimilation in a latent space, for both smoothing and filtering.

We introduce the problem of learning priors for smoothing and for filtering in Section
8.1
.
The following two sections discuss general methodologies for solving this problem in the context of smoothing.
We develop two approaches both based on maximum likelihood estimation of the parameters of the prior dynamics model.
Section
8.2
describes the expectation–maximization (EM) framework, developed
in a general setting in Chapter
15
, and applied here to estimate parameters entering the prior (dynamics)
model; as a by-product we obtain a method for joint parameter and state estimation.
In particular, we will present practical algorithmic instances of the EM framework to estimate parameters defining
the dynamics map and/or the error covariance.
Section
8.3
discusses auto-differentiable Kalman filters, an approach to maximum likelihood estimation
that can be much cheaper than the EM framework, but applies primarily
when a linear observation operator is used since it invokes a Gaussian approximation to estimate the state.
The method relies on Kalman-type algorithms to approximate the likelihood function, and on
auto-differentiation to approximate its gradient. In Section
8.4
we discuss the learning of prior distributions
for each step of the filtering problem, considering the use of empirical priors and Gaussian priors
constructed from the outcome of the preceding step of the filtering process. Section
8.5
discusses
the idea of working in a latent space for data assimilation algorithms. Section
8.6
closes with
extensions and bibliographical remarks.

Remark 8.1
.

Learning priors for data assimilation is intimately related to the important problem of jointly estimating the state and parameters of a stochastic dynamics model; this problem is ubiquitous in data assimilation, and
arises in a variety of situations.
For example, the model form may be known from first principles, but specific values of parameters may need to be learned from data.
Or the model form may not be known perfectly and machine learning may be used to find a correction of the model which enhances
its accuracy. Finally, when a model is expensive, it may be desirable to learn a cheap-to-evaluate machine-learned surrogate, as discussed in Section
6.6
. Learning the model also arises when the dynamics of the system that generates the data are totally unknown
and a data-driven model is sought. A correction, a surrogate or a data-driven model may be represented by
a neural network, random features,
or Gaussian process model, for example; see Chapter
12
for more detail on these types of
function approximation classes; see also Chapter
14
for classes of functions designed for data-driven temporal forecasting.
∎

8.1
Learning Priors for Smoothing and Filtering: Problem Setting

Consider the stochastic dynamics and observation models given by

(8.1a)

\displaystyle v^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\vartheta}(v^{\dagger}_{j})+\xi^{\dagger}_{j},

\displaystyle\hskip 9.95863pt\xi^{\dagger}_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)\text{ i.i.d.},

(8.1b)

\displaystyle y^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=h(v^{\dagger}_{j+1})+\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1},

\displaystyle\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Gamma)\text{ i.i.d.},

with
v^{\dagger}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
and the noise/initial data independence structure summarized in Assumption
6.1
.
Here,
\vartheta
are unknown parameters entering the definition of the dynamics map
\Psi_{\vartheta}
and we assume the error covariance
\Sigma
to be also, in general, unknown.
We denote by
\theta\coloneqq\{\vartheta,\Sigma\}
the collection of unknown parameters; it is possible to extend to parameterizations of
the covariance
\Sigma
, rather than to learn the entire covariance matrix, as discussed in Remark
6.33
, but we do not pursue this here. For a given and fixed integer
J,
we recall the following notation, from Subsection
6.1.1
, for the signal
and observations:

V^{\dagger}:=\{v^{\dagger}_{0},\ldots,v^{\dagger}_{J}\},\quad\quad Y^{\dagger}:=\{y^{\dagger}_{1},\ldots,y^{\dagger}_{J}\}.

Note that
V^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{(J+1)d}
and
Y^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{Jk}
.
We make the following data assumption:

Data Assumption 8.2
.

Data
Y^{\dagger}
is given and assumed to come from the model (
8.1
).

8.1.1
Smoothing

Given data
Y^{\dagger}
we wish to recover the parameter
\theta
defining the
dynamics model. In Chapter
12
we study the supervised learning
problem of determining a map such as
\Psi_{\vartheta}
from data of the form

\bigl\{u^{(n)},\Psi_{\vartheta}(u^{(n)})\bigr\}_{n=1}^{N}

where the inputs
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
are generated i.i.d. from an unknown probability density function; see Data Assumption
12.1
.
Here we only have access to observations
Y^{\dagger}
obtained from indirect and noisy measurement of an unknown trajectory
V^{\dagger}.

We aim to choose the parameters of the dynamics model, such as
\vartheta
and also
\Sigma
, which will explain an unknown trajectory
V^{\dagger}
consistent, according to the observations model, with the observations
Y^{\dagger}
that we are given.
We are hence going beyond the standard supervised learning setting with i.i.d. input data, considered in Chapter
12
.
This idea underlies the EM and auto-differentiable Kalman filtering algorithms introduced in the next two sections.

8.1.2
Filtering

Recall that the filtering distribution cycles between predicting with the (stochastic) dynamics model (
6.1
) and solving an inverse problem defined by the
data model (
6.2
). The prediction and
analysis steps of the filtering cycle are summarized in (
6.4
). The analysis step
itself corresponds to solving the Bayesian inverse problem for
v^{\dagger}_{j+1}
, given
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
, where

(8.2)

y^{\dagger}_{j+1}=h(v^{\dagger}_{j+1})+\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}.

The prior on
v^{\dagger}_{j+1}
is
\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}
and the noise is
\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Gamma).

Filtering is intrinsically harder than a standard Bayesian inverse problem, however, in the sense
that the prior
\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}
is typically not available exactly. For this reason, many algorithms used in practice employ either learned priors on the state to facilitate the analysis step, or learned joint distributions on the state and observation, also to facilitate the analysis step.

8.1.3
Motivating Examples

Before presenting the algorithms to learn priors for smoothing and for filtering, we consider three motivating examples,
building on the discussion in Remark
8.1
.

Example 8.3

(Parameterized Dynamics)
.

The state dynamics may be governed by the flow of a parameterized system of differential equations, and we may be interested in estimating a parameter
\vartheta
entering the definition of the vector field in the differential equations from data
Y^{\dagger}.

∎

Example 8.4

(Model Correction)
.

The dynamics may be unknown, but we may still have access to an inaccurate model
\Psi^{\text{approx}}
. Here
\vartheta
can represent, for example, the parameters of a neural network, random features, or Gaussian process
\Psi^{\text{correction}}_{\vartheta}
used to correct the inaccurate model
\Psi^{\text{approx}}
. The goal is to learn
\vartheta
so that
\Psi_{\vartheta}:=\Psi^{\text{approx}}+\Psi^{\text{correction}}_{\vartheta}
approximates the true dynamics of the state. Learning model corrections is important in applications where available models have moderate predictive accuracy.
For instance, fine scales of the state may not be resolved accurately due to computational constraints, and we may be interested in learning a surrogate model from data which accounts for the unresolved scales of the system.
More generally, non-additive model corrections may also be learned.
∎

Example 8.5

(Fully Unknown Dynamics)
.

The state dynamics may be fully unknown and
\vartheta
may represent the parameters of, for example, a neural network, random features,
or Gaussian process surrogate model
\Psi_{\vartheta};
see Chapter
12
for background on these parameterizations of functions. The goal is to find an accurate surrogate model
\Psi_{\vartheta}
. Even if the true dynamics are known, a surrogate model, or
emulator
, can be cheaper to evaluate, enabling the use of large sample sizes for particle filters or ensemble Kalman methods; see Section
6.6
. Similar computational considerations motivate learning the forward map for inverse problems in
Section
1.6
.
∎

8.2
Expectation Maximization

In this section we work under Data Assumption
8.2
, and so have access only to data
Y^{\dagger}
obtained from indirect and noisy measurement of a signal trajectory.
We view the problem of estimating
\theta\coloneqq\{\vartheta,\Sigma\}\in\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
from
data
Y^{\dagger}
as a
missing data
problem.
Ideally we estimate
\theta
from both
V^{\dagger}
and
Y^{\dagger}
, but the data
V^{\dagger}
is missing. So it is natural that
any algorithm used will also estimate
V^{\dagger}
from
Y^{\dagger}.

We adopt a

maximum likelihood
approach to the problem of estimating
\theta
from
Y^{\dagger}
: we aim to maximize
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger}|\theta).
The premise of the methodology we now introduce is that evaluation of the likelihood function
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger}|\theta)
is challenging due to the missing, or
latent
, variable
V^{\dagger}
, but that the joint distribution
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger},V^{\dagger}|\theta)

is readily available. With this in mind we seek to address the problem of missing data by employing
the identities

(8.3a)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger}|\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{(J+1)d}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger},V^{\dagger}|\theta)\,dV^{\dagger},

(8.3b)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger},V^{\dagger}|\theta)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger}|V^{\dagger},\theta)\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(V^{\dagger}|\theta).

Remark 8.6
.

Likelihood functions that are computationally expensive to evaluate, such as the integral in (
8.3
),
motivate the introduction of likelihood-free methods. These are discussed for Bayesian inference in Chapter
5
.
The methods use only samples of the joint distribution on state and observation, rather than evaluations of
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger}|\theta)
; see Subsection
5.2.3
. In this chapter, we will focus on optimization algorithms, rather than Bayesian methods, to find point estimators for the parameter
\theta
.
∎

Our working assumption, then, is that the likelihood
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger}|\theta)
is intractable;
but that it is expressible by marginalization of the tractable extended likelihood
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger},V^{\dagger}|\theta)
as
in (
8.3
a); and that, furthermore,
for the dynamics-observation model (
8.1
), this extended likelihood is readily computed using conditioning as in (
8.3
b).
In this setting the maximum likelihood estimator of
\theta
can be computed by employing the
expectation–maximization
(EM) algorithm described in Section
15.5
. Specifically, in the notation of that section, we consider parameter
u:=\theta,
missing variable
z:=V^{\dagger}
and data
y:=Y^{\dagger}.

We employ the EM Algorithm
5
, using
\ell
to index the algorithmic iterations because
j
, the notation used in Chapter
15
, is used here to index time in the dynamics-observation model (
8.1
). The extended likelihood
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger},V^{\dagger}|\theta)

needed in order to implement the EM algorithm can be readily computed from the dynamics and observation models in (
8.1
), using
the identity (
8.3
b):

(8.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger},V^{\dagger}|\theta)&=\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger}|V^{\dagger},\theta)+\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(V^{\dagger}|\theta)=\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger}|V^{\dagger})+\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(V^{\dagger}|\theta)\\
&=\mathsf{const}-\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle{\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}}\bigl|y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-h(v^{\dagger}_{j+1})\bigr|_{\Gamma}^{2}-\frac{J}{2}\log\det(\Gamma)\\
&\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\bigl|v^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\Psi_{\vartheta}(v^{\dagger}_{j})\bigr|^{2}_{\Sigma}-\frac{J}{2}\log\det(\Sigma)-\frac{1}{2}|v^{\dagger}_{0}-m_{0}|^{2}_{C_{0}},\end{split}

where
\mathsf{const}
is a constant independent of
\theta
.

We recall that the E-step in the EM Algorithm
5

integrates the
full
log-likelihood
\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger},V^{\dagger}|\theta)
in (
8.4
) with respect to
q^{\ell}(V^{\dagger})=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(V^{\dagger}|Y^{\dagger},\theta^{\ell})
.
Note that
q^{\ell}(V^{\dagger})
is the solution of the smoothing problem, with parameter of the stochastic dynamics set at
\theta^{\ell}.

Then
\theta^{\ell+1}
is computed in the M-step by maximizing the expectation resulting from this integration, with respect to

\theta:

\theta^{\ell+1}\in\argmax_{\theta\in\Theta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{(J+1)d}}\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger},V^{\dagger}|\theta)q^{\ell}(V^{\dagger})\,dV^{\dagger}.

The resulting maximization problem, dropping from the objective terms that do not depend on
\theta
, is as follows:

(8.5a)

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}(\theta;q^{\ell})

\displaystyle=-\frac{J}{2}\log\det(\Sigma)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{(J+1)d}}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\bigl|v^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\Psi_{\vartheta}(v^{\dagger}_{j})\bigr|^{2}_{\Sigma}\,q^{\ell}(V^{\dagger})\,dV^{\dagger},

(8.5b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\ell+1}

\displaystyle\in\argmax_{\theta}\mathsf{L}(\theta;q^{\ell}).

Noting that
q^{\ell}(V^{\dagger})=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(V^{\dagger}|Y^{\dagger},\theta^{\ell})
depends on
\theta^{\ell}
, we see that this algorithm defines
a mapping
\theta^{\ell}\mapsto\theta^{\ell+1}.
The mapping is defined by solving the smoothing problem, with stochastic
dynamics parameter set at
\theta^{\ell}
and then optimizing (
8.5
a) to find
\theta^{\ell+1}.

To illustrate the application of the EM algorithm, in the next Subsection
8.2.1

we discuss a simplified setting in which we learn only the model error covariance
\Sigma,
assuming a known dynamics map
\Psi.

This enables closed-form solution of (
8.5
). Then, in Subsection
8.2.2
we return to the problem of learning
\theta=\{\vartheta,\Sigma\}.
Finally, in Subsection
8.2.3
we consider the application of the EM algorithm in a model correction setting with known error covariance
\Sigma,
and make a connection to supervised learning.

8.2.1
Learning Model Error Covariance

In this subsection we consider the setting where the dynamics map
\Psi
is known, but the dynamics error covariance
\Sigma
is unknown. That is, we have
\theta=\Sigma
.
In this setting, we notice that the loss function (
8.5
) in the M-step
has the same form as the objective in Proposition
2.8
for an
unknown covariance in a Gaussian variational inference problem. Thus, the loss can be
explicitly maximized over
\Sigma
. Setting
\theta^{\ell}=\Sigma^{\ell}
,
and using the explicit optimizer, we find the covariance matrix updates

(8.6)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\ell+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{J}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{(J+1)d}}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\Big(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\Psi(v^{\dagger}_{j})\Big)\otimes\Big(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\Psi(v^{\dagger}_{j})\Big)\,q^{\ell}(V^{\dagger})\,dV^{\dagger};

recall that
q^{\ell}(V^{\dagger})=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(V^{\dagger}|Y^{\dagger},\Sigma^{\ell})
solves the smoothing problem,
here with stochastic dynamics noise covariance
\Sigma^{\ell}.
The following result shows that this update
for the parameter
\Sigma
leads to a monotonic increase of the log-likelihood for the observed data
Y^{\dagger}
.

Theorem 8.7
.

Suppose that the parameter space for
\Sigma
is the set of symmetric positive-definite matrices and that, for every
\ell\geq 0,

(8.7)

A^{\ell}\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{(J+1)d}}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\Bigl(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\Psi(v^{\dagger}_{j})\Bigr)\otimes\Bigl(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\Psi(v^{\dagger}_{j})\Bigr)q^{\ell}(V^{\dagger})\,dV^{\dagger}

is positive definite. Then the M-step has the unique solution

(8.8)

\Sigma^{\ell+1}=\frac{1}{J}A^{\ell},

and the resulting EM iterates satisfy

\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger}|\Sigma^{\ell+1})\geq\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger}|\Sigma^{\ell}).

Proof.

For fixed
q^{\ell},
the part of the M-step objective that depends on
\Sigma
is

\mathsf{L}(\Sigma;q^{\ell})=-\frac{J}{2}\log\det(\Sigma)-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}\bigl(\Sigma^{-1}A^{\ell}\bigr).

Introducing the precision matrix
\Omega=\Sigma^{-1}
gives

\mathsf{L}(\Omega;q^{\ell})=\frac{J}{2}\log\det(\Omega)-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}\bigl(\Omega A^{\ell}\bigr).

This function is strictly concave in
\Omega,
and its first-order optimality condition is

J\Omega^{-1}=A^{\ell}.

Hence its unique maximizer is

\Sigma^{\ell+1}=\Omega^{-1}=\frac{1}{J}A^{\ell}.

The conclusion now follows from the monotonicity property of the EM algorithm in Theorem
15.30
.
∎

Remark 8.8
.

See the discussion in Remark
15.31
highlighting the fact that
additional assumptions are required for convergence of the EM iteration
to a local maximizer of the likelihood. Furthermore, in practice we will only have samples from an empirical approximation of
q^{\ell}(V^{\dagger})=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(V^{\dagger}|Y^{\dagger},\Sigma^{\ell})
and so we may update the covariances using Monte Carlo approximations of the covariance matrices in (
8.6
). With these Monte Carlo approximations the monotonic increase in the likelihood is no longer guaranteed.
∎

8.2.2
Monte Carlo EM

Now we return to the general setting of finding parameter
\theta=\{\vartheta,\Sigma\}
. Given (approximate) samples
\{(V^{\dagger})^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
from the smoothing distribution
q^{\ell}(V^{\dagger})=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(V^{\dagger}|Y^{\dagger},\theta^{\ell}),
we may use
Monte Carlo to approximate the expectation in the loss function (
8.5
). We thus
obtain the empirical approximation

(8.9)

\mathsf{L}^{N}(\theta;q^{\ell})=-\frac{J}{2}\log\det(\Sigma)-\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\bigl|(v^{\dagger}_{j+1})^{(n)}-\Psi_{\vartheta}\bigl((v^{\dagger}_{j})^{(n)}\bigr)\bigr|^{2}_{\Sigma}.

Note that the objective function depends on
\theta^{\ell}
through the smoothing samples
\{(V^{\dagger})^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}.

As in the preceding subsection, we notice that, given
\vartheta,
the expression (
8.9
) can be explicitly maximized over
\Sigma.
Indeed, the optimal solution is given by the covariance matrix

(8.10)

\Sigma^{\ell+1}=\frac{1}{NJ}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\Big((v^{\dagger}_{j+1})^{(n)}-\Psi_{\vartheta}\bigl((v^{\dagger}_{j})^{(n)}\bigr)\Big)\otimes\Big((v^{\dagger}_{j+1})^{(n)}-\Psi_{\vartheta}\bigl((v^{\dagger}_{j})^{(n)}\bigr)\Big);

this expression may also be viewed as a Monte Carlo approximation of
identity (
8.6
).
We note that this covariance depends on the parameters
\vartheta
via
the model
\Psi_{\vartheta}.
To maximize the loss
\mathsf{L}(\theta;q^{\ell})
over
\theta=\{\vartheta,\Sigma\}
, we can employ iterative optimization methods: alternate between optimization over
\Sigma
,
for fixed
\vartheta
, as given in the preceding subsection, and optimization
over
\vartheta
. Algorithm
3
summarizes the resulting EM-type algorithm, for learning both states and
parameters of a dynamical system, using Monte Carlo and gradient ascent updates and, in particular,
equations (
8.9
) and (
8.10
).
We notice that this implementation of the EM framework treats the
parameters
\vartheta
and
\Sigma
differently in the M-step in order
to exploit the closed-form covariance update. Specifically, for each
fixed
\vartheta,
we maximize the empirical M-step objective explicitly
over
\Sigma,
obtaining
\Sigma^{\ell+1}(\vartheta).
Substituting this
expression into the objective defines the profiled objective

{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\vartheta),
which we then approximately maximize using gradient
ascent. Thus, the M-step is solved exactly with respect to
\Sigma
for
each fixed
\vartheta,
but only approximately with respect to

\vartheta.
Finally, the number of gradient ascent steps
\mathcal{I}

may depend on
\ell,
although it is fixed in
Algorithm
3
for ease of presentation.

Algorithm 3

Expectation–Maximization with Monte Carlo and Gradient Ascent

1:

Input
: Initialization
\theta^{0}=\{\vartheta^{0},\Sigma^{0}\}.
Rule for gradient ascent step-sizes
\{\alpha^{i}\}_{i=1}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

2:

For
\ell=0,1,\ldots,L-1
do the following expectation and maximization steps:

3:

E-Step
: Obtain (approximate) samples
\{(V^{\dagger})^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
from
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(V^{\dagger}|Y^{\dagger},\theta^{\ell})
.

4:

M-Step
: Using the samples obtained in the E-step, define

\Sigma^{\ell+1}(\vartheta):=\frac{1}{NJ}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\Bigl((v^{\dagger}_{j+1})^{(n)}-\Psi_{\vartheta}\bigl((v^{\dagger}_{j})^{(n)}\bigr)\Bigr)\otimes\Bigl((v^{\dagger}_{j+1})^{(n)}-\Psi_{\vartheta}\bigl((v^{\dagger}_{j})^{(n)}\bigr)\Bigr)

and

{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\vartheta):=-\frac{J}{2}\log\det\bigl(\Sigma^{\ell+1}(\vartheta)\bigr)-\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\Bigl|(v^{\dagger}_{j+1})^{(n)}-\Psi_{\vartheta}\bigl((v^{\dagger}_{j})^{(n)}\bigr)\Bigr|_{\Sigma^{\ell+1}(\vartheta)}^{2}.

5:

Initialize
\vartheta^{\ell,0}=\vartheta^{\ell}.

6:

for

i=0,\dots,\mathcal{I}-1

do

7:

\vartheta^{\ell,i+1}=\vartheta^{\ell,i}+\alpha^{i}D_{\vartheta}{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\vartheta^{\ell,i}).

8:

end

for

9:

Set
\vartheta^{\ell+1}=\vartheta^{\ell,\mathcal{I}}
and
\Sigma^{\ell+1}=\Sigma^{\ell+1}(\vartheta^{\ell+1}).

10:

Output
: Approximation
\theta^{L}
to the maximum likelihood estimate for
\theta
.

Remark 8.9
.

The smoothing distribution
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(V^{\dagger}|Y^{\dagger},\theta^{\ell})
for the signal
\{v^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{J}
can be approximated by performing Bayesian inversion or data assimilation methods,
using the stochastic dynamics and data models in (
8.1
) with learned parameter
\vartheta^{\ell}
and covariance model
\Sigma^{\ell}.
These samples may be obtained, for instance, using Markov chain Monte Carlo methods.
Alternatively, the ensemble Kalman smoother described in Subsection
6.4.4
may be employed,
noting, however, that it is only guaranteed to
yield accurate posterior samples in approximately linear–Gaussian settings.
Note that approximate filters could also be used; however, the theory underpinning
EM does require use of the smoothing distribution. Nonetheless
the computational expediency of filtering can be appealing and does allow for a biased approximation of the required missing data.
∎

Remark 8.10
.

In analogy to the supervised learning task studied in Chapter
12
, the objective function introduced in equation (
8.5
) can be conceptually interpreted as defining a risk, that is, an expected loss. Here, expectation is with respect to the
smoothing distribution
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(V^{\dagger}|Y^{\dagger},\theta^{\ell})
which represents our available knowledge of the latent variable
V^{\dagger}

given data
Y^{\dagger}
and parameter estimate
\theta^{\ell}.
Then, (
8.9
) can be interpreted as an empirical risk, defined via approximate samples
(V^{\dagger})^{(n)}\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(V^{\dagger}|Y^{\dagger},\theta^{\ell}).

∎

Remark 8.11
.

While we present Algorithm
3
using gradient ascent, in practice we may employ other optimization methods such as accelerated first-order methods or second-order methods, like Gauss–Newton, to optimize the parameter
\vartheta
; we refer to Chapter
15
for more details. The ideal choice of algorithm will depend on computational constraints and specific large- and fine-scale properties of the loss function.
∎

8.2.3
Correcting Model Error Using Analysis Increments

Here we assume the model error covariance
\Sigma
to be known, and consider the problem of learning the parameter
\theta:=\vartheta
in the dynamics map model correction setting introduced in Example
8.4
. That is, we consider a parameterization of the dynamics map of the form

(8.11)

\Psi_{\vartheta}=\Psi^{\text{approx}}+\Psi_{\vartheta}^{\text{correction}},

where
\Psi^{\text{approx}}
is a known approximation to the dynamics map and we seek to find a
correction
\Psi_{\vartheta}^{\text{correction}}.
In this setting, there is a natural connection between the EM framework and supervised learning. Specifically, approximating
q^{\ell}(V^{\dagger})
in the E-step using trajectories
\{(V^{\dagger})^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
computed with a data assimilation algorithm (filtering or smoothing, see Remark
8.9
), we obtain an empirical approximation of the loss function in (
8.5
) of the form

(8.12)

\mathsf{L}^{N}(\theta;q^{\ell})=c-\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\Bigl|(v^{\dagger}_{j+1})^{(n)}-\Psi^{\text{approx}}\bigl((v^{\dagger}_{j})^{(n)}\bigr)-\Psi_{\vartheta}^{\text{correction}}\bigl((v^{\dagger}_{j})^{(n)}\bigr)\Bigr|_{\Sigma}^{2};

here
c
