is a constant that is independent of the parameter
\theta=\vartheta
to be estimated.
We recognize that maximizing (
8.12
) over
\theta
corresponds to the supervised learning problem of estimating the mapping from
v^{\dagger}_{j}
to the
analysis increments

v^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\Psi^{\text{approx}}(v^{\dagger}_{j})
given (approximate) analysis samples
(V^{\dagger})^{(n)}
.

Remark 8.12
.

The Monte Carlo samples
\{(V^{\dagger})^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
of the time-series may have been created without the model error correction; this amounts to assuming
\Psi^{\text{approx}}
is the true dynamics model for the purpose of the data assimilation, in which case the loss in
(
8.12
) is simply
\mathsf{L}^{N}(\theta)
(no dependence on
q^{\ell}
) and EM is no longer needed. Alternatively, they
may have been created from the corrected model for a setting
\vartheta^{\ell}
that was found in an earlier iteration of the EM algorithm. In the latter case, the maximization
of
\mathsf{L}^{N}(\cdot;q^{\ell})
yields
\theta^{\ell+1}.

∎

8.3
Auto-Differentiable Kalman Filters

Consider again the state–observation model defined by (
8.1
), and make the additional assumption that
the observation operator is linear:
h(v)=Hv
for some matrix
H\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
. We again consider the
problem of estimating the unknown parameters of the dynamics model,
\theta=\{\vartheta,\Sigma\}
, under Data Assumption
8.2
,
as in the preceding section. But here we consider a different algorithmic framework, based on two ideas: firstly approximating the log-likelihood
\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger}|\theta)
using Kalman-based filters; and secondly computing its derivatives via automatic differentiation.
The goal is to perform (approximate) gradient ascent on the log-likelihood function; we refer to Section
15.2
for background on gradient descent (in this section
ascent
as we seek to
maximize
the likelihood) and to Section
15.7
for background on automatic differentiation. We will illustrate the idea of approximating the log-likelihood function using Kalman-based filters by focusing on the extended Kalman filter (ExKF). However
the methodology directly generalizes to the ensemble Kalman filter (EnKF)
and other Kalman-based filtering algorithms – see Remark
8.14
. In Subsection
8.3.1
we recall the ExKF;
and in Subsection
8.3.2
we show how this filter may be used to execute approximate maximum likelihood estimation
for
\theta.

8.3.1
The Extended Kalman Filter

Recall from Subsection
6.3.4
that the ExKF is based on a Gaussian approximation of the prediction and analysis distributions given by (
6.27
) and
(
6.28
). Here we apply the same ExKF methodology to the
\theta
-dependent
dynamics–observation model (
8.1
), resulting in the approximations

(8.13)

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j},\theta)\approx\mathcal{N}\bigl(\widehat{m}_{j+1}(\theta),\widehat{C}_{j+1}(\theta)\bigr)

for the predictive distribution and

(8.14)

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\theta)\approx\mathcal{N}\bigl(m_{j+1}(\theta),C_{j+1}(\theta)\bigr)

for the analysis distribution. Comparing with (
6.27
) and
(
6.28
), the reader will note that we have (intentionally) switched to
m_{j}
and

\widehat{m}_{j+1}
for the ExKF mean and predictive mean,
and we have made explicit the dependence of computed means and covariances on the parameter

\theta.
We now describe the resulting algorithm.

We set

(8.15)

\displaystyle A_{j}(\theta)

\displaystyle:=D\Psi_{\vartheta}\bigl(m_{j}(\theta)\bigr).

The mean and covariance update according to the formulae

(8.16a)

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{j+1}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\vartheta}\bigl(m_{j}(\theta)\bigr),

(8.16b)

\displaystyle m_{j+1}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\widehat{m}_{j+1}(\theta)+K_{j+1}(\theta)\bigl(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-H\widehat{m}_{j+1}(\theta)\bigr).

Here the gain
K_{j+1}(\theta)
is computed as part of the computation of
the covariance evolution, as in (
6.31
):

(8.17a)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{j+1}(\theta)

\displaystyle=A_{j}(\theta)C_{j}(\theta)A_{j}(\theta)^{\top}+\Sigma,

(8.17b)

\displaystyle K_{j+1}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{j+1}(\theta)H^{\top}\bigl(H\widehat{C}_{j+1}(\theta)H^{\top}+\Gamma\bigr)^{-1},

(8.17c)

\displaystyle C_{j+1}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\bigl(I-K_{j+1}H\big)\widehat{C}_{j+1}(\theta).

8.3.2
Approximate Maximum Likelihood Estimation

In this subsection we show how the Gaussian ansatz (
8.13
), and the formulae (
8.15
), (
8.16
),
and (
8.17
), can be leveraged to produce an ExKF-based approximation of the log-likelihood function
\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger}|\theta)
. This leads to an algorithm for maximum likelihood estimation of parameters
\theta
in the stochastic dynamics model.
For the derivation presented below, we assume that the observation operator is linear, so that the predictive distribution of each observation is Gaussian whenever the predictive state distribution is Gaussian. Specifically,
the derivation rests on the following characterization of the log-likelihood function under a Gaussian ansatz.

Theorem 8.13
.

Assume that the observation
operator is linear:
h(\cdot)=H\cdot.

Suppose that, for each
0\leq j\leq J-1,
the predictive distribution

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j},\theta)
of the stochastic dynamics and data models (
8.1
) is Gaussian with mean
\widehat{m}_{j+1}(\theta)
and covariance
\widehat{C}_{j+1}(\theta).
Then the log-likelihood function admits the following characterization:

(8.18a)

\displaystyle\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger}|\theta)=

\displaystyle\,{\mathsf{J}}(\theta),\quad S_{j+1}(\theta)=H\widehat{C}_{j+1}(\theta)H^{\top}+\Gamma,

(8.18b)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)\coloneqq

\displaystyle\mathsf{const}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\bigl|y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-H\widehat{m}_{j+1}(\theta)\bigr|_{S_{j+1}(\theta)}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\log\det\bigl(S_{j+1}(\theta)\bigr),

where
\mathsf{const}
is independent of
\theta
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger}|\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j},\theta),

where we use the convention that
Y^{\dagger}_{0}:=\emptyset

so that conditioning on
Y^{\dagger}_{0}
does not provide any information.
Now conditioning in the data model

y^{\dagger}_{j+1}=Hv^{\dagger}_{j+1}+\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1},

we see that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j},\theta\bigr]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[Hv^{\dagger}_{j+1}+\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j},\theta\bigr]=H\widehat{m}_{j+1}(\theta),

\displaystyle\text{Cov}\bigl[y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j},\theta\bigr]

\displaystyle=\text{Cov}\bigl[Hv^{\dagger}_{j+1}+\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j},\theta\bigr]=H\widehat{C}_{j+1}(\theta)H^{\top}+\Gamma.

Moreover,
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j},\theta)
is Gaussian. The result follows.
∎

Thus, for any value of
\theta
, we may obtain an
approximation
to the log-likelihood
\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{\dagger}|\theta)
by running an
extended Kalman filter, obtaining predictive means and covariances
\bigl(\widehat{m}_{j+1}(\theta),\widehat{C}_{j+1}(\theta)\bigr),
for
0\leq j\leq J-1
, and using (
8.18a
). Moreover, an approximation of the log-likelihood gradient at
\theta
can be obtained
by auto-differentiating through our extended Kalman-based likelihood approximation. Auto-differentiable Kalman filters use these estimates of the log-likelihood gradients to conduct gradient ascent. The procedure is summarized in Algorithm
4
.

Algorithm 4

Auto-Differentiable Kalman Filter

1:

Input
: Initialization
\theta^{0},
rule to choose step-sizes
\{\alpha^{\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{L-1}.

2:

For
\ell=0,1,\ldots,L-1
do the following Kalman filtering and gradient ascent steps:

3:

Kalman Filtering
: Run the extended Kalman filtering algorithm defined in Subsection
8.3.1
to obtain predictive means and covariances
\widehat{m}_{j+1}(\theta^{\ell}),\widehat{C}_{j+1}(\theta^{\ell}),
for
0\leq j\leq J-1.

4:

Gradient Ascent:
Auto-differentiate the map
\theta\mapsto{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)
defined
in (
8.18b
) to obtain a gradient estimate
D{\mathsf{J}}(\theta^{\ell}).
Set

(8.19)

\displaystyle\theta^{\ell+1}=\theta^{\ell}+\alpha^{\ell}D{\mathsf{J}}(\theta^{\ell}).

5:

Output
: Approximation
\theta^{L}
to the maximum likelihood estimate for
\theta
.

Remark 8.14
.

The algorithm may be generalized from the ExKF to the EnKF, using (
6.38
a) and (
6.41a
) for the predicted mean and covariance of the EnKF.
If desired, at the conclusion of the algorithm, the signal
\{v^{\dagger}_{j}\}
can be estimated by performing data assimilation (ExKF or EnKF) with the stochastic dynamics and data models in (
8.1
) with learned parameters
\theta^{L}.
As with Remark
8.11
, although we present Algorithm
4
using gradient ascent, other optimization methods may be used instead.
∎

Remark 8.15
.

Notice that in the EM Algorithm
3
each posterior sample
\{(V^{\dagger})^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
is used to perform
\mathcal{I}
gradient ascent
steps in the M-Step, with the goal of maximizing the lower bound
\mathcal{L}(q^{\ell},\theta).
In contrast, in the auto-differentiable Kalman filter Algorithm
4
, each run of a Kalman filtering algorithm is used to produce an estimate of the log-likelihood gradient, and a gradient ascent step is taken.
This is possible because we can analytically integrate out the state variable,
given a linear observation operator and Gaussian predictive distribution.
∎

8.4
Learning Priors for the Filtering Step from Samples

The preceding two sections have focused on learning the prior
for the smoothing problem, namely the stochastic dynamics model,
in the setting described in Subsection
8.1.1
. In this section we study
learning the prior, and generalizations, for the filtering problem,
in the setting described in Subsection
8.1.2
. The previous sections also focus on
learning a fixed set of parameters defining the dynamics model, while here we discuss learning a prior distribution much more generally
and at every analysis time.

We start by describing how particle-based filtering methods can be seen as learning a prior for the analysis step in the filtering cycle, in both
Subsections
8.4.1
and
8.4.2
. These methods are all based on
learning, from samples, either a non-parametric or
parametric distribution for the prior used in the analysis step;
the resulting prior is either an empirical measure (non-parametric) or
a Gaussian (parametric) measure and in either case is found from a form of density estimation (see Subsection
13.1
). We also show, in Subsection
8.4.2
, that certain
algorithms are based not on learning the prior, but on learning the joint distribution
on the state–observation pair.

8.4.1
Bootstrap Particle Filter

For particle-based methods the prior, or the joint distribution on state and observation, must be reconstructed from the predicted ensemble of particles
\{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}.

For the bootstrap particle filter we reconstruct a particle approximation to
\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}
, namely

(8.20)

\widehat{\pi}^{{\mathsf{app}}}_{j+1}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}}.

This is an elementary form of learning the prior from data, as explained in Chapter
13
,
and equation (
13.2
) in particular.
The Bayesian inverse problem defined by (
8.2
) may be solved exactly with this prior, to obtain
the posterior

(8.21)

\pi^{{\mathsf{app}}}_{j+1}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}w_{j+1}^{(n)}\delta_{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}}.

The weights, defined by the likelihood from (
8.2
), are given in (
6.50
).
Thus we have shown that the bootstrap particle filter has at its
core a form of learning the prior, in this case construction of an empirical measure from the predicted samples.

8.4.2
Ensemble Kalman Filter

Now consider the setting where the particles
\{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
are derived from
the prediction step of the EnKF. Initially we consider the setting in which
h(\cdot)=H\cdot
is linear, then generalize to nonlinear
h
.
In the setting of linear observation map, the inverse problem defining the analysis step is to find
v^{\dagger}_{j+1}
given
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
where

(8.22)

y^{\dagger}_{j+1}=Hv^{\dagger}_{j+1}+\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}.

Recall the notation for
{\mathsf{G}}
, the projection onto Gaussian measures, defined in Remark
2.13
.
To define the EnKF we learn the Gaussian prior

(8.23)

\widehat{\pi}^{{\mathsf{app}}}_{j+1}={\mathsf{G}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}}\Bigr).

We obtain
\widehat{\pi}^{{\mathsf{app}}}_{j+1}=\mathcal{N}\bigl(\widehat{m}_{j+1},\widehat{C}_{j+1}\bigr)
, where

(8.24a)

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{n=1}\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},

(8.24b)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{n=1}\bigl(\widehat{v}^{(n)}_{j+1}-\widehat{m}_{j+1}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\widehat{v}^{(n)}_{j+1}-\widehat{m}_{j+1}\bigr).

Thus we have adopted a different form of learning the prior, in comparison to the
bootstrap particle filter, since we have formed a Gaussian. If we solve the linear inverse problem defined by (
8.22
), for
v^{\dagger}_{j+1}
given
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
, using this Gaussian prior, we obtain a Gaussian posterior. Approximate samples from this Gaussian posterior are given by

(8.25a)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}+K_{j+1}\bigl(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\eta_{j+1}^{(n)}-H\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}\bigr),\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(8.25b)

\displaystyle K_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{j+1}H^{\top}\bigl(H\widehat{C}_{j+1}H^{\top}+\Gamma\bigr)^{-1},

where
\eta_{j+1}^{(n)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Gamma)
are drawn i.i.d. with respect to particle index
n
, as well as time-index
j.

This simply defines the EnKF analysis step from Subsection
6.3.6
, in the linear case
described in Remark
6.13
. Thus, in the case of
linear
H
, the EnKF can be thought of as being derived from using a learned Gaussian prior in every analysis step, and then sampling the posterior, before cycling back to the prediction step.

Similar ideas may be applied when the observation operator
h
is
nonlinear, but instead of learning the
prior
in the
Gaussian class, we learn a Gaussian approximation
of the
joint distribution
of the predicted state and observation.
To this end, we define the joint distribution

(8.26)

\widehat{\gamma}_{j+1}={\mathsf{G}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}}\otimes\mathcal{N}\bigl(h(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}),\Gamma\bigr)}}\right).

We have again used the notation
{\mathsf{G}},
the projection onto Gaussian
measures defined in Remark
2.13
. The mean of

\widehat{\gamma}_{j+1}
is

(8.27)

\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{m}_{j+1}\\
\widehat{h}_{j+1}\end{pmatrix},

and its covariance is

(8.28)

\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{C}_{j+1}&\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{vh}\\
\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{hv}&\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{hh}+\Gamma\end{pmatrix},

where

(8.29a)

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},

(8.29b)

\displaystyle\widehat{h}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}h\bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}\bigr),

(8.29c)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{vh}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}-\widehat{m}_{j+1}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(h(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)})-\widehat{h}_{j+1}\bigr),

(8.29d)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{hh}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bigl(h(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)})-\widehat{h}_{j+1}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(h(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)})-\widehat{h}_{j+1}\bigr).

Here
\widehat{C}_{j+1}
is given by (
8.24
b), and

\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{hv}=(\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{vh})^{\top}.

Conditioning the Gaussian approximation
\widehat{\gamma}_{j+1}
on
the observed value
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
motivates the perturbed-observation
EnKF update

(8.30a)

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=h\bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}\bigr)+\eta_{j+1}^{(n)},\qquad\eta_{j+1}^{(n)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Gamma),

(8.30b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}+K_{j+1}\bigl(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)}\bigr),\qquad n=1,\ldots,N,

(8.30c)

\displaystyle K_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{vh}\bigl(\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{hh}+\Gamma\bigr)^{-1}.

The variables
\eta_{j+1}^{(n)}
are drawn independently across
the ensemble index
n
and time index
j.
The update
(
8.30
) is the EnKF analysis step from
Subsection
6.3.6
, as given by equations
(
6.37
b) and (
6.38
). Thus, when
h
is
nonlinear, the EnKF can be viewed as learning a Gaussian approximation
of the joint distribution of the predicted state and observation,
conditioning this approximation on the observed data, and using the
resulting conditional Gaussian structure to update the ensemble before
cycling back to the prediction step.

8.5
Data Assimilation in a Latent Space

In this section we perform data assimilation in a latent space representation of the state space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}
and
g:\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

be an encoder–decoder pair, as discussed in Section
13.5
.
Thus
g\circ f\approx\mathrm{Id}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}.
The pair
(f,g)
define an autoencoder.
If
g
is invertible, we can take
f=g^{-1}
.
We now express the likelihood and prior, and consequently the posterior, in terms of the latent space variables. There are some similarities with the ideas underlying Section
3.3
where we reformulate the inverse problem in a latent space for variable
z
; in that section, however, we primarily focus on the setting
d_{z}=d
whereas here our primary motivation is the setting where
d_{z}\ll d.

We start by making the following assumption concerning available data:

Data Assumption 8.16
.

The dynamics model (
6.1
a) has an invariant measure
\mu.

We are given a trajectory
\{v^{\rm train}_{1},\ldots,v^{\rm train}_{N}\}

generated by the dynamics model (
6.1
a)
with
v^{\rm train}_{0}\sim\mu
.

For each integer
n
in
\{1,\cdots,N\}
, the point
v^{\rm train}_{n}
is drawn from
\mu
, albeit not independently. Using this data for training,
and following techniques such as the variational autoencoder described in Chapter
13
, we obtain a map
g
that approximately pushes forward a latent space distribution
\zeta
on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}
to
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
; that is,
g_{\sharp}\zeta\approx\mu
. We also assume that map
f
approximately pushes forward
\mu
to the latent space:
f_{\sharp}\mu\approx\zeta
.

Remark 8.17
.

We note that although
\zeta
will not directly be used as a prior in the data assimilation problem, it is a natural latent
reference distribution for the model (
8.1
), since it represents the model statistics through its invariant measure.
∎

We now proceed to show how
g
and
f
can be used to perform smoothing and filtering in the latent space.

8.5.1
Smoothing in a Latent Space

Adapting the idea of latent space representation contained in
Equation
3.12
, and the definition of the smoothing distribution
in Subsection
6.2.2
, we define
Z^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{(J+1)d_{z}}

and recall
V^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{(J+1)d}:

\displaystyle Z^{\dagger}

\displaystyle:=\{z^{\dagger}_{0},z^{\dagger}_{1},\ldots,z^{\dagger}_{J}\},

\displaystyle V^{\dagger}

\displaystyle:=\{v^{\dagger}_{0},v^{\dagger}_{1},\ldots,v^{\dagger}_{J}\}.

We then also define

\mathsf{g}:\mathbb{R}^{(J+1)d_{z}}\to\mathbb{R}^{(J+1)d}
and

\mathsf{h}:\mathbb{R}^{(J+1)d}\to\mathbb{R}^{Jk}
by

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}(Z^{\dagger})

\displaystyle:=\bigl\{g(z^{\dagger}_{0}),g(z^{\dagger}_{1}),\ldots,g(z^{\dagger}_{J})\bigr\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{h}(V^{\dagger})

\displaystyle:=\bigl\{h(v^{\dagger}_{1}),\ldots,h(v^{\dagger}_{J})\bigr\}.

The likelihood for the smoothing problem in the latent space is then
given by

\mathsf{l}_{g}(Y^{\dagger}|Z^{\dagger})\propto\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2}\bigl|Y^{\dagger}-\mathsf{h}(\mathsf{g}(Z^{\dagger}))\bigr|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2}\Bigr).

We then define a prior in the latent space as

\rho_{g}(Z^{\dagger})\propto\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2}|g(z^{\dagger}_{0})-m_{0}|_{C_{0}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}|g(z^{\dagger}_{j+1})-\Psi\bigl(g(z^{\dagger}_{j})\bigr)|_{\Sigma}^{2}\Bigr),

leading to the latent space posterior

\Pi^{Y^{\dagger}}_{g}\!(Z^{\dagger})\propto\mathsf{l}_{g}(Y^{\dagger}|Z^{\dagger})\rho_{g}(Z^{\dagger}).

We then have the posterior in the original space given approximately by

\Pi^{Y^{\dagger}}\!(V^{\dagger})\approx\mathsf{g}_{\sharp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi^{Y^{\dagger}}_{g}\!(Z^{\dagger})}}\right).

Remark 8.18
.

We note that the latent-space prior is not the pullback of the original prior, even if
g
is invertible.
This is because the change of variables from
V^{\dagger}
to
Z^{\dagger}
introduces determinants of the Jacobian of the change of variables. So, in the posterior approximation, there are two approximations: one arising from the fact that
g
is only an approximate push-forward; and a second, modeling, approximation that may be harder to justify. This observation also applies to the discussion of MAP estimation (4DVar) in what follows below, since MAP estimators are not invariant under nonlinear reparameterization.
∎

Example: 4DVar in a Latent Space

4DVar is derived as the maximum
a posteriori
(MAP) estimate of the smoothing distribution; see Subsection
6.4.2
.
To reduce the complexity of solving the maximization problem in
\mathbb{R}^{(J+1)d}
we introduce the idea of finding a MAP estimator for the latent space posterior, in the smaller space
\mathbb{R}^{(J+1)d_{z}}.
This leads to the following optimization problem:

(8.31a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(Z^{\dagger};Y^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|g(z^{\dagger}_{0})-m_{0}|_{C_{0}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}|g(z^{\dagger}_{j+1})-\Psi\bigl(g(z^{\dagger}_{j})\bigr)|_{\Sigma}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle{\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}}|y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-h(g(z^{\dagger}_{j+1}))|_{\Gamma}^{2};

(8.31b)

\displaystyle Z_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{Z^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{(J+1)d_{z}}}{\mathsf{J}}(Z^{\dagger};Y^{\dagger}).

To obtain the state estimate
V_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}
in the original space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define

(8.32)

V_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}=\mathsf{g}(Z_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}).

Remark 8.19
.

If
d_{z}\ll d
, there may be significant computational gains from carrying out the 4DVar optimization problem (
8.31b
) in the latent space rather than in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as in (
6.70
), which motivates this approach. Note, however, that the cost of evaluating
the decoder map
g(\cdot)
must be factored in.
∎

8.5.2
Filtering in a Latent Space

Thus far we have used only the decoder
g
; this suffices for the smoothing problem
and for its attendant MAP estimator. For filtering we will
also need the encoder
f
, allowing us to perform prediction in the original state space,
then encode to perform the analysis step in the latent space.
Recalling the definition of the filtering distribution in Subsection
6.2.1
, we may define the latent space analysis operator
{\mathsf{A}}_{g}(\cdot;y)
by

{\mathsf{A}}_{g}(\pi;y)(z)=\frac{\mathsf{l}_{g}(y|z)\pi(z)}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}}\mathsf{l}_{g}(y|z)\pi(z)\,dz},

where

\mathsf{l}_{g}(y|z)\propto\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{2}\bigl\lvert y-h(g(z))\bigr\rvert_{\Gamma}^{2}}}\right).

We now define the modified prediction–analysis cycle, wherein the analysis step is performed in the latent space while the prediction step (
6.6
) is unchanged:

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\text{ \bf Prediction Step:}}~~~~\;&(\widehat{\pi}_{g})_{j+1}={\mathsf{P}}(\pi_{g})_{j}.\\
{\text{ \bf Latent Analysis Step:}}~~~~~&(\pi_{g})_{j+1}=g_{\sharp}{\mathsf{A}}_{g}(f_{\sharp}(\widehat{\pi}_{g})_{j+1};y^{\dagger}_{j+1}).\end{split}

Then,
(\pi_{g})_{j}\approx\pi_{j}
, where
\pi_{j}=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})
is the true filtering distribution. Note that
\pi_{g}
, like
\pi
, is
in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Example: EnKF in a Latent Space

We start by defining an observation operator that maps from the latent space to the observation space,
h_{g}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
:

h_{g}=h\circ g.

To obtain the ensemble
\{v_{j+1}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
from
\{v_{j}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
, we first perform the forecast step, then map into the latent space, perform the EnKF update, and map back into the original space:

(8.33a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j}^{(n)})+\xi^{(n)}_{j},\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(8.33b)

\displaystyle\widehat{z}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=f(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}),\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(8.33c)

\displaystyle z_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\widehat{z}_{j+1}^{(n)}+K_{j+1}\bigl(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\eta_{j+1}^{(n)}-h_{g}(\widehat{z}_{j+1}^{(n)})\bigr),\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(8.33d)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=g(z_{j+1}^{(n)}),\quad n=1,\ldots,N.

Here
\xi_{j}^{(n)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma),\eta_{j+1}^{(n)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Gamma)
, as before.

The gain matrix
K_{j+1}
is calculated according to

(8.34a)

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{n=1}\widehat{z}_{j+1}^{(n)},

(8.34b)

\displaystyle\widehat{h}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{n=1}h_{g}(\widehat{z}_{j+1}^{(n)}),

(8.34c)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{zh}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{n=1}\bigl(\widehat{z}^{(n)}_{j+1}-\widehat{m}_{j+1}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(h_{g}(\widehat{z}^{(n)}_{j+1})-\widehat{h}_{j+1}\bigr),

(8.34d)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{hh}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{n=1}\bigl(h_{g}(\widehat{z}^{(n)}_{j+1})-\widehat{h}_{j+1}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(h_{g}(\widehat{z}^{(n)}_{j+1})-\widehat{h}_{j+1}\bigr),

(8.34e)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{yy}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{hh}+\Gamma,

(8.34f)

\displaystyle K_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{zh}\bigl(\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{yy}\bigr)^{-1}.

Remark 8.20
.

As with the latent space 4DVar, the latent space EnKF may be computationally beneficial when
d_{z}\ll d
. In this setting, moreover, it may suffer less from the problems of sampling error discussed in Subsection
6.3.6
.
∎

Remark 8.21
.

To avoid having to map back into
\mathbb{R}^{d}
after the analysis step and apply the possibly costly forward dynamics
\Psi
, one can instead learn a surrogate
\Psi^{\prime}_{g}
and noise covariance matrix
\Sigma_{g}^{\prime}
for the dynamics in the latent space. This can be done by mapping the data given in Data Assumption
8.16
to the latent space, using
the encoder, and then using the methods discussed in Chapter
14
for time-series forecasting to learn a dynamics model in the latent space; see also Section
6.6
for the use of surrogates in data assimilation. Then, the latent forecast ensemble can be given directly by

\displaystyle\widehat{z}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\Psi^{\prime}_{g}(z_{j}^{(n)})+(\xi_{g}^{\prime})^{(n)}_{j},\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

\displaystyle(\xi_{g}^{\prime})^{(n)}_{j}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma^{\prime}_{g}),\quad n=1,\ldots,N.

∎

8.6
Bibliography

In this chapter we have introduced two computational frameworks for joint state and parameter estimation: the EM algorithm and auto-differentiable filters. Both frameworks find the parameters in the dynamical system by approximating the maximum likelihood estimator, and then use a filtering or smoothing algorithm to recover the state. We have focused on learning the dynamics model, but both frameworks directly generalize to learning the observation model; thus, the EM algorithm and auto-differentiable filters can be used not only to learn the prior distribution for the smoothing problem, but also the likelihood function. This final section provides bibliographical context for the algorithms considered in this chapter, and briefly discusses other computational methods that do not stem from a maximum likelihood formulation.

Embedding of the EnKF and the ensemble Kalman smoother (EnKS) into the EM algorithm was proposed in
[
469
,
479
,
150
,
392
]
, with a focus on estimation of error covariance matrices. The E-step is approximated with an EnKS under the Monte Carlo EM framework
[
503
]
. In addition,
[
83
,
362
,
506
]
incorporate machine learning techniques in the M-step to train neural network surrogate models or corrections. The paper
[
71
]
proposes Bayesian estimation of model error statistics, together with a neural network emulator for the dynamical system. On the other hand,
[
480
,
468
,
129
]
consider online EM methods for error covariance estimation with EnKF. Online methods aim to reduce computation by not reprocessing the smoothing distribution for each new observation. Although gradient information is used during the M-step to train the surrogate models in
[
83
,
362
,
71
]
, these methods do not auto-differentiate through the EnKF. Other methods to learn observation and model error covariances are considered in
[
339
,
61
,
497
,
341
]
.

Auto-differentiable Kalman filters were introduced in
[
121
]
, which proposes and analyzes an approach for joint state and parameter estimation that leverages gradient information of an EnKF estimate of the likelihood. A unified framework for co-learning state, dynamics, and filtering algorithms in data assimilation using auto-differentiable Kalman methods was developed in
[
4
]
. The paper
[
122
]
considers learning a latent low-dimensional surrogate model for the dynamics and a decoder that maps from the latent space to the state space using auto-differentiable Kalman filters.
EnKFs for derivative-free maximum likelihood estimation are studied in
[
454
,
392
]
. An empirical comparison of the likelihood computed using the EnKF and other filtering algorithms is made in
[
99
]
; see also
[
225
,
344
]
. The paper
[
151
]
uses EnKF likelihood estimates to design a pseudo-marginal MCMC method for Bayesian inference of model parameters. The works
[
452
,
453
]
propose online Bayesian parameter estimation using the likelihood computed from the EnKF under a certain family of conjugate distributions.

While our discussion has focused on ensemble Kalman methods, particle filters can also be employed for joint state and parameter estimation. Particle filters give an unbiased estimate of the data likelihood
[
144
,
23
]
. Based upon this likelihood estimate, a particle MCMC Bayesian parameter estimation method is designed in
[
23
]
. Although particle filter likelihood estimates are unbiased, they suffer from two potential drawbacks that limit their applicability to some problems. First, their variance can be large, as they inherit the weight degeneracy of importance sampling in high dimensions
[
443
,
6
,
429
,
425
]
—see Chapter
6
for further background on this subject. Second, while the forecast and analysis steps of particle filters can be auto-differentiated, the resampling steps involve discrete distributions that cannot be handled by the reparameterization trick, as discussed in
[
121
]
. For this reason, previous differentiable particle filters omit auto-differentiation of the resampling step
[
357
,
326
,
308
]
, introducing a bias.

An alternative to maximum likelihood estimation is to optimize a lower bound on the data log-likelihood with variational inference
[
68
,
282
,
401
]
. The posterior distribution over the latent states is approximated with a parametric distribution and is jointly optimized with model parameters defining the stochastic dynamics model. In this direction, variational sequential Monte Carlo methods
[
357
,
326
,
308
]
construct the lower bound using a particle filter. Moreover, the proposal distribution of the particle filter is parameterized and jointly optimized with model parameters.
Although variational sequential Monte Carlo methods provide consistent data log-likelihood estimates, they suffer from the same two potential drawbacks as likelihood-based particle filter methods. A recent work
[
260
]
proposes blending variational sequential Monte Carlo and EnKF with an importance sampling-type lower bound estimate, which is effective if the state dimension is small. Other works that build on the variational inference framework include
[
290
,
402
,
178
,
332
]
. An important challenge is to obtain suitable parameterizations of the posterior, especially when the state dimension is high. For this reason, a restrictive Gaussian parameterization with a diagonal covariance matrix is often used in practice
[
290
,
178
]
. The topic of variational approximations to filtering and smoothing problems is discussed in Chapter
7
.

Another alternative approach to maximum likelihood estimation is to concatenate state and parameters into an augmented state-space and employ the data assimilation methods, from other
chapters in this Part II, in the augmented state-space. This approach requires one to design a pseudo-dynamic for the parameters, which can be challenging when certain types of parameters (e.g., error covariance matrices) are involved
[
452
,
145
]
or if the dimension of the parameters is high.
The use of the EnKF for jointly learning the state and model parameters by
state augmentation
was introduced in
[
20
]
.

Beyond the problem of joint parameter and state estimation, the development of data-driven frameworks for learning dynamical systems is a very active research area. We refer to
[
312
]
for a framework and to
[
89
,
209
,
210
,
228
,
399
]
for recent methods that do not rely on the EM algorithm, auto-differentiation of filtering methods, or variational inference. In addition, data-driven machine learning frameworks have gained popularity for combining physics-based models with model error corrections. These hybrid models may avoid the over-smoothing or unphysical behavior of predictive models that replace the entire forecast dynamics
[
77
]
. Two common representations for model error include additive corrections
[
165
,
76
]
and data-driven subgrid scale parameterizations
[
403
,
75
]
; we refer to Section
1.5
for other forms of model error. We note that the approach discussed in this chapter learns the corrections offline, as online methods typically require the adjoint operator of the physics-based model, which may be challenging to obtain for some physics-based models, e.g., in numerical weather prediction and for climate modeling
[
321
]
.

Model error correction using analysis increments is considered in
[
137
,
165
,
117
]
. Alternatively,
machine learning can be used to account for model error in data assimilation by learning a mapping between the attractor defined by the model and the attractor of the true system; see
[
7
]
for one approach. Another approach to correcting for model error in weather forecasting is known as
post-processing
; in particular, it is common to post-process ensemble forecasts. See
[
483
]
for a survey of approaches, as well as
[
247
]
for an approach using permutation-invariant neural networks.

Performing data assimilation in the latent space of an autoencoder (Section
13.5
) or variational autoencoder (Section
13.6
) is considered in

[
384
,
122
,
340
,
375
]
.
Recent application to problems with shocks may be found in
[
521
,
107
]
.

Chapter 9
Transport Perspective on Filters

This chapter is devoted to the transport perspective on filtering; its content is therefore related to
Chapter
4
concerning transport methods for
inverse problems. Here, the term
transport
refers broadly to a map designed to represent aspects of the analysis step within the filtering update (
6.4
). To see the connection with Chapter
4
, recall from Subsection
6.2.1
that the analysis step can in fact be formulated as an inverse problem. The transport framework for filtering developed in this chapter encompasses methods for both probabilistic and state estimation as defined in Subsection
6.1.3
. In probabilistic estimation the aim is to approximate the analysis map
{\mathsf{A}}
itself; in state estimation the aim is to determine a single point estimate for the solution of the inverse problem defined by
{\mathsf{A}}.

We start with the dynamics and data model (
6.1
) and (
6.2
), repeated here for convenience. Consider initialization
v^{\dagger}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
and, for
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},
let

(9.1a)

\displaystyle v^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v^{\dagger}_{j})+\xi^{\dagger}_{j},

(9.1b)

\displaystyle y^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=h(v^{\dagger}_{j+1})+\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}.

The assumptions on the initial condition and the noise sequences are as detailed in Assumption
6.1
.
Recall Definition
6.4
of the filtering problem
and the notation for
V^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger}_{j}
introduced in Subsection
6.1.1
. We are interested in designing algorithms to learn about
v^{\dagger}_{j}
, given all the data observed up to time
j
,
Y^{\dagger}_{j}.
In this chapter we will study algorithms for estimating the state,
\{v^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
, or for approximating the filtering distribution on the state,

\{\pi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}.
In both cases we consider sequential algorithms with respect to
j
, and refer to these collectively as
filters
.
The transport algorithms we consider take the form of mappings from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(or multiple copies thereof) into itself. We will parameterize these mappings and learn the parameters from training data.
These learnable mappings are
based on the prediction–analysis
cycle (
6.4
); we also briefly consider the
modified prediction–analysis structure underlying
the optimal particle filter from Subsection
6.3.8
.

Generalizing 3DVar

We start by formulating a class of state estimation algorithms of the form,
for
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,

(9.2a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j})+s\xi_{j},

(9.2b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}

\displaystyle=T\bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1};y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\theta\bigr).

The algorithm is initialized at
v_{0}
, typically sampled from Gaussian

\mathcal{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
, the known distribution of
v^{\dagger}_{0}.

We allow for both
s=0
and
s=1
, as in our formulation of 3DVar in (
6.23
). We will primarily use this form of state estimation algorithm with
s=0
but use of
s=1
, with
\xi_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
an i.i.d. sequence, may also be considered. In the context of state estimation, the objective is to choose
\theta\in\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}

so that
V=\{v_{j}\}_{j=0}^{J}
determined by (
9.2
) approximates well the signal
V^{\dagger}=\{v_{j}\}_{j=0}^{J}
given by the dynamics model (
9.1
). Notice that
v_{j}
defined by (
9.2
) depends only on observations
Y^{\dagger}_{j}
up to time
j.
Consequently, the sequence
V=\{v_{j}\}_{j=0}^{J}
provides a
filter
for state estimation.

Generalizing EnKF

We now introduce ensemble filters that can be used for either
state estimation
or probabilistic estimation.
Motivated by the EnKF in form (
6.42
), we generalize
(
9.2
) and consider a particle-based map of the form,
for
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,

(9.3a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j}^{(n)})+\xi^{(n)}_{j},\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(9.3b)

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=h(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)})+\eta^{(n)}_{j+1},\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(9.3c)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=T\Bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)},y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(\ell)},\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(\ell)}\}_{\ell=1}^{N};\theta\Bigr),\quad n=1,\ldots,N.

The recursion may be initialized, for instance, by independent samples
v_{0}^{(n)}\sim\mathcal{N}(m_{0},C_{0}).

The noise defining this random dynamical system satisfies Assumption
6.11
.
For any given integer
n
the resulting
noise sequences over
j
are thus distributionally equivalent
to the noise sequences appearing in (
9.1
),
as detailed in Assumption
6.11
.
The collection
\{{v}_{j}^{(\ell)}\}_{\ell=1}^{N}

defines the empirical measure

(9.4)

\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}(\theta)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\delta_{v_{j}^{(\ell)}}.

We may define a state estimator
v_{j}(\theta)
by taking the mean with respect to this
probability measure:

(9.5)

v_{j}(\theta)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}v_{j}^{(\ell)}.

For each integer
1\leq n\leq N
, the step (
9.3
a) defines a Markov operator
{\mathsf{P}}
,
identical to the one defining the prediction step in (
6.4
).
Furthermore, under (
9.3
a),
\mathbb{P}(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}|{v}_{j}^{(n)})
is Gaussian. As a consequence of (
9.4
) it follows
that, conditioned on a given ensemble at time
j,

{\mathsf{P}}\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}
is a Gaussian mixture.
The points
\{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(\ell)}\}_{\ell=1}^{N}
then comprise samples
from this Gaussian mixture. Later it will be useful to consider the empirical measure
defined by these samples:

(9.6)

\widehat{\pi}^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j+1}(\theta)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\delta_{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(\ell)}}.

Remark 9.1
.

Recall mapping
\pi\to\gamma_{\pi}
defined by (
6.7
).
Then the points

\{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(\ell)},\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(\ell)}\}_{\ell=1}^{N}

comprise samples from
\gamma_{{\mathsf{P}}\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}}
and
can be used to define the empirical measure

(9.7)

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\delta_{(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(\ell)},\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(\ell)})}.

∎

The empirical measures (
9.6
) and (
9.7
)
are invariant under permutations of the ensemble from
which they are formed. We assume henceforth
that dependence of the map
T(\cdot)
on

\{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(\ell)},\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(\ell)}\}_{\ell=1}^{N}

is also invariant under permutations in index
\ell.
To be specific, the map in (
9.3c
) is assumed to take the form (abusing notation)

(9.8)

T\Bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)},y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(\ell)},\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(\ell)}\}_{\ell=1}^{N};\theta\Bigr)=T\Bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)},y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\delta_{(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(\ell)},\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(\ell)})};\theta\Bigr);

thus, the map depends on the joint empirical measure (
9.7
)
of the states and observations.

Remark 9.2
.

It may also be of interest to work with a non-stochastic form of generalized
ensemble Kalman filter. We describe several examples of this, adopting the
idea illustrated in (
9.8
) to enforce ensemble permutation invariance.
For instance we might modify (
9.3
) and consider

(9.9a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j}^{(n)})+\xi^{(n)}_{j},\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(9.9b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=T\Bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},h\bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}\bigr),y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\delta_{\bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(\ell)},h(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(\ell)})\bigr)};\theta\Bigr),\quad n=1,\ldots,N.

The noise defining this random dynamical system again satisfies Assumption
6.11
.
The following simpler special case of (
9.9
) is also useful for expository purposes:

(9.10a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j}^{(n)})+\xi^{(n)}_{j},\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(9.10b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=T\Bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\delta_{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(\ell)}};\theta\Bigr),\quad n=1,\ldots,N.

∎

Remark 9.3
.

We refer to an algorithm such as (
9.3
), (
9.9
) or (
9.10
), leading to a candidate approximation of the filtering distribution in the form (
9.4
), as an
equal weights ensemble filter
. Recall from Subsection
6.3.9
that weighted particle filters, such as the
bootstrap
and optimal particle filters, are prone to weight collapse; this motivates the wish to find equally weighted filters.
∎

Remark 9.4
.

The proposed learnable algorithms (
9.2
), (
9.3
), (
9.9
)
and (
9.10
) all allow for the possibility of learning dependence on the observed data

y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
at each analysis step. Learning such dependence is feasible
when the stochastic dynamical system defined by coupling the underlying signal dynamics to
the filtering algorithm, through the observations, is ergodic.
(See Subsection
9.2.5
for discussion of ergodicity for filtering algorithms.)
This is feasible because of a form of amortization
over time, exploiting ergodicity present in the filtering problem.
In Chapter
10
we go beyond amortization for filtering through multiple
time indices, and consider use of multiple observation
sequences

Y^{\dagger}
which can
enhance learning of the dependence of each analysis step on
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
. The discussion in this
remark is closely related to that for variational inference in Remark
7.7
.
∎

We may choose
\theta
either so that the resulting algorithm performs well at
state estimation or so that it
matches the filtering distribution.
We may think of the map
T
as a transport map,
as introduced in Section
13.2
. This nomenclature
is most commonly used in the mathematics literature to describe maps
that, through the pushforward operation, map one probability measure into another.
We will use this terminology, however, in the context of both state
estimation and filtering. We think of the map
T
as transporting states,
predicted by the underlying stochastic dynamical system, so that they match the data.
In Section
9.1
we describe a variety of
learning objectives that may be used so that this transport performs well at state estimation. Section
9.2

then deploys these objectives to learn optimal parameters in specific
problems classes of the form (
9.2
) or (
9.3
), and their generalizations, such as (
9.9
) and (
9.10
), for the
purposes of state estimation.
In Section
9.3
we describe various objectives
that may be used so that these transport algorithms perform well at matching the filtering distribution – probabilistic estimation.
Section
9.4
describes algorithm classes that can be learned using
these objectives defined for probabilistic estimation; we
concentrate on ensemble-based methods.
Finally, Section
9.5

contains bibliographical remarks.

9.1
State Estimation: Learning Objectives

This section defines a variety of learning objectives that are relevant
for state estimation. We work primarily under the following data assumption
concerning a data stream in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Data Assumption 9.5
.

We have access to the data set
\{v^{\dagger}_{j},y^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
,
generated as a realization of (
9.1
).

Notice that here
\{v^{\dagger}_{j},y^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
represents
training data
used to learn the parameters of filtering algorithms. The interpretation of these sequences is therefore slightly different than in previous chapters, in which
\{y^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
represents given observations used to estimate unknown signal
\{v^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{J}.

In this chapter we will use training data from Data Assumption
9.5
to learn filtering algorithms; these algorithms can be deployed on a new set of observations to estimate the unknown signal that underlies them. The training data from Data Assumption
9.5

enables us, potentially, to learn good choices of the parameters defining the filters.
Recall that we discussed training data in the context of
amortized inference,
for the Bayesian inverse problem, in Chapter
5
.
In the next remark we discuss the sense
in which amortization applies in this setting where we simply have access
to a single realization from the joint state and observation model.

Remark 9.6
.

If the filter is viewed as an architecture that maps the entire sequence of observations

Y^{\dagger}
to the entire sequence of states
V^{\dagger}
, while respecting the causal structure of these sequences in time, then utilizing Data Assumption
9.5
could be viewed as solving a supervised learning problem with only one data pair, a data-poor learning environment. However, embedded in the solution to this problem is the learning of a single transport map that uses the observation at each step
j
to perform filtering sequentially: the analysis step
from (
6.4
). Since the methodology uses only one element of the observation
sequence at a time then if
J
is large we may view this as a data-rich learning environment for this single transport map. For this to indeed be a data-rich learning environment requires some form
of statistical similarity in the transport problem, independently of
j
; then
for large
J
the learning problem will indeed be data rich. This data rich environment may arise, for instance, under ergodicity of the signal and observation processes; we will discuss ergodicity of filtering algorithms in
Remark
9.12
and in Subsection
9.2.5
.
∎

As an alternative to Data Assumption
9.5
we may simply assume that we have access to data

\{y^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
and that this data is related to an unobserved signal process
\{v^{\dagger}_{j}\}
with unknown dynamics. Because the dynamics (
9.1
a) are unknown we do not have access to

\{v^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{J}
. In this setting we will use the observation operator
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
to map the states into the observation space, to define a loss from which to learn parameters of the filtering algorithm. In this setting we work under the following:

Data Assumption 9.7
.

We have access to data set
\{y^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
,
generated as a realization of (
9.1
).

The remainder of this section is organized as follows. Subsection
9.1.1
focuses on state estimation using deterministic
scoring rules.
Subsection
9.1.2
goes beyond
this setting by working with probabilistic scoring rules
that compare the distribution defined by an ensemble algorithm with the true
state. In both Subsection
9.1.1
and Subsection
9.1.2
we employ Data Assumption
9.5
. In Subsection
9.1.3
we discuss objective functions defined in observation space rather than state space; this is useful when model error is significant and we work under Data Assumption
9.7
.

9.1.1
Matching the State: Deterministic Scoring Rule

Consider an algorithm, such as (
9.2
) or (
9.3
), which gives rise to an estimator
v_{j}=v_{j}(\theta)
for
v^{\dagger}_{j}
; for example
v_{j}(\theta)
may be
v_{j}
itself for algorithm (
9.2
) or the ensemble average
v_{j}
in (
9.5
), for algorithm (
9.3
). Given Data Assumption
9.5
we may then apply a deterministic scoring rule
\mathsf{d}(\cdot,\cdot)
, satisfying Definition
11.81
, to measure the distance between
sequences
\{v^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
and
\{v_{j}(\theta)\}_{j=1}^{J}
. An optimal
\theta^{\star}
may then
be chosen to minimize this distance:

(9.11a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{J}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathsf{d}\bigl(v_{j}(\theta),v^{\dagger}_{j}\bigr),

(9.11b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}^{J}(\theta).

Note that
v_{j}(\theta)
will, in order to respect the causal structure of filtering, depend on the observed data
Y^{\dagger}_{j}=\{y^{\dagger}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{j}
; thus when run to
J
steps the state estimator
has seen the entire observation sequence
Y^{\dagger}=\{y^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
. Consequently
\theta^{\star}
will, through the optimization procedure, depend on
the entirety of the data specified in Data Assumption
9.5
. The optimization problem defined by (
9.11
) may be performed using auto-differentiation with respect to
\theta
; see Section
15.7
for details on this methodology. If
J
is large we may exploit
some form of ergodicity to learn an algorithm which can be deployed
with different observed data streams.

Example 9.8
.

The canonical example of a distance-like deterministic scoring
rule
is
\mathsf{d}(v,w)=|v-w|^{2}
, the squared Euclidean distance. If this choice is made,
then (
9.11
a) is called the
mean-square error
,
and its square root the
root-mean-square error
(RMSE).
See Remark
11.55
, and the material preceding
it, connecting the squared Euclidean distance to probabilistic scoring rules.
∎

Remark 9.9
.

In Subsection
10.1.3
we show how working with an
amortized
version of (
9.11
), in the specific case of the squared Euclidean distance and averaged over multiple realizations of the state and data,
leads to an objective function that is minimized at the true filter mean.
∎

9.1.2
Matching the State: Probabilistic Scoring Rule

We now go beyond simply matching a state estimator (from an algorithm) with the true state of the system (from (
9.1
a)). Instead, we look at algorithms that output a probability distribution over states, and use a scoring rule to measure
the distance of that distribution from the true state of the system.
Assume that the algorithm class of interest produces an estimator of the filtering
distribution
\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}(\theta)\approx\pi_{j}.

An example of such an approximation is
\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}(\theta)
defined by (
9.3
), (
9.4
). We aim to choose
\theta
so as to minimize the score
between
\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}(\theta)
and the true signal
v^{\dagger}_{j}
underlying the data.

To this end, we employ a probabilistic scoring rule
\mathsf{S}(\cdot,\cdot)
,
introduced in Section
11.3
, to obtain

(9.12a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{J}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathsf{S}(\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}(\theta),v^{\dagger}_{j}),

(9.12b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}^{J}(\theta).

This may be implemented under Data Assumption
9.5
.
We emphasize that it is important, in order to be able to implement this
optimization problem, to use scoring rules which can be evaluated when
\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}(\theta)
is given as an empirical measure. Scoring rules with this
property include the energy score, from Definition
11.51
.
On the other hand, the logarithmic score, from Definition

11.70
, cannot be used with
empirical measures. In summary, the optimization problem in (
9.12
) can be implemented with ensemble-based methods if the scoring rule is chosen appropriately.

Remark 9.10
.

In Section
10.3
we show how working with an amortized version of (
9.12
), averaged over multiple realizations of the state and data,
leads to objective functions that are minimized at the true filtering distribution.
∎

9.1.3
Matching the Data

We now assume access only to data

\{y^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
, a natural setting when the dynamics (
9.1
a) are unknown
so we do not have access to
\{v^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{J}
. We work under Data Assumption
9.7
and
consider the following objective function defined in the observation space:

(9.13)

{\mathsf{J}}^{J}(\theta)=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathsf{d}\Bigl(h\bigl(v_{j}(\theta)\bigr),y^{\dagger}_{j}\Bigr).

Once again we typically choose
v_{j}(\theta)
to be
v_{j}
itself, given by (
9.2
), or the ensemble average given by (
9.3
), (
9.5
).

We note that
{\mathsf{J}}^{J}(\theta)
is then a proxy for the loss with respect to the true trajectory in the observation space:

(9.14)

\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathsf{d}\Bigl(h\bigl(v_{j}(\theta)\bigr),h(v^{\dagger}_{j})\Bigr).

The difference between the two losses is that (
9.13
) contains observational noise in the observation, which may be substantial, and there is a risk to overfitting to this noise. The following remark addresses this issue.

Remark 9.11
.

Optimizing the cost function
{\mathsf{J}}^{J}(\theta)
in (
9.13
) does not always lead to good performance. The root cause is that the objective function can overfit to the specific observations, which contain unknown observational errors. For example, if
h
is uniquely invertible then a perfect score can be achieved by simply setting
v_{j}(\theta)=h^{-1}(y^{\dagger}_{j})
. When the signal-to-noise ratio is small,
this will lead to a very noisy estimate
v_{j}(\theta)
of the signal. If only a single realization of the observed process is available, then this is a fundamental obstacle. However, if it is possible to obtain multiple independent realizations,
then the overfitting issue can be mitigated. In this setting, we describe a method based on this observation to avoid overfitting. We take the squared Euclidean distance
\mathsf{d}(u_{1},u_{2})=|u_{1}-u_{2}|^{2}
as the scoring rule and analyze a single time
j
. Similar analyses could be performed for other scoring rules and multiple times.

Recall that
y^{\dagger}_{j}=h(v_{j}^{\dagger})+\eta_{j}^{\dagger}
.
Note that the data assimilation algorithm produces an estimator
v_{j}(\theta)

for
v^{\dagger}_{j}
which will depend on
y^{\dagger}_{j}
and hence on
\eta^{\dagger}_{j}.
We consider the contribution to the
objective function in (
9.13
) at
j
and take expectation

\mathbb{E}
over independent realizations of the observation noise
\eta^{\dagger}_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Gamma)
. This yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl|h\bigl(v_{j}(\theta)\bigr)-y^{\dagger}_{j}\bigr|^{2}=\mathbb{E}\bigl|h(v_{j}(\theta))-h(v^{\dagger}_{j})\bigr|^{2}+\mathrm{Tr}(\Gamma)-2\mathbb{E}\Bigl[h\bigl(v_{j}(\theta)\bigr)^{\top}\eta^{\dagger}_{j}\Bigr].

Rearranging we obtain

(9.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl|h(v_{j}(\theta))-h(v^{\dagger}_{j})\bigr|^{2}+\mathrm{Tr}(\Gamma)=\mathbb{E}\bigl|h\bigl(v_{j}(\theta)\bigr)-y^{\dagger}_{j}\bigr|^{2}+2\mathbb{E}\Bigl[h\bigl(v_{j}(\theta)\bigr)^{\top}\eta^{\dagger}_{j}\Bigr].

Minimizing the left-hand side is desirable as it matches the output of the
analysis,
v_{j}(\theta)
, with the true signal,
v^{\dagger}_{j},
in observation
space; note that it does
not depend on the observational noise explicitly, only through the
dependence of
v_{j}(\theta)
on the data. The right-hand side
can be approximated through sampling the observational noise
\eta^{\dagger}_{j}
,
and then optimizing over
\theta
to approximately minimize the left-hand side.
1
1

1

The term
\mathbb{E}\Bigl[h(v_{j}(\theta))^{\top}\eta^{\dagger}_{j}\Bigr]
is
sometimes referred to as the
optimism
.

The natural setting in which the idea explained here may be used is when
Data Assumption
9.7
is generalized to multiple realizations of an
observation stream defined over
j\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
.
∎

9.2
State Estimation:
Algorithms

Our interest in this section is in learning transport-based
filtering algorithms for state estimation.
We study learning within the context of specific forms of
algorithms (
9.2
) and (
9.3
), or their generalizations. In particular, we consider algorithms designed by modifying three specific algorithms from Chapter
6
: 3DVar, the EnKF, and the optimal particle filter. We work under
Data Assumption
9.5
. Subsection
9.2.1
is devoted to learning the
gain in 3DVar. In Subsection
9.2.2
we study the same question,
but in the context of generalizations of the EnKF. Subsection
9.2.3

also studies generalizations of the EnKF, but focuses on learning localization and
inflation parameters. In Subsection
9.2.4
we go beyond ensemble algorithm classes of the form in (
9.3
),
showing how the optimal particle filter
may be used as the basis to learn new
algorithms for equally weighted ensemble methods.

9.2.1
Learning the Gain in 3DVar

Recall the 3DVar algorithm (
6.23
), repeated here for convenience:

(9.16a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j}),

(9.16b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{j+1}+K\bigl(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-h(\widehat{v}_{j+1})\bigr).

This is a specific subclass of the algorithm class defined in (
9.2
). To fully specify the 3DVar algorithm we need to choose the gain matrix
K
. We investigate learning
\theta:=K
on the basis of Data Assumption
9.5
. (We note that, alternatively,
K
could be parameterized as
K=K(\theta)
, and the parameter
\theta
could be learned, but we do not pursue this here.) For the dynamics/data model underlying Data Assumption
9.5
and for the 3DVar algorithm (
9.16
), we make the same Gaussian and independence assumptions on the initialization and noise as made in equations (
6.1
), (
6.2
) and Assumption
6.2
. In particular, we assume that
v^{\dagger}_{0}
and
v_{0}

are drawn from the same distribution
\mathcal{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
, but independently.

Linear
h
: The Original Derivation of 3DVar

Consider the linear setting where
h(v)=Hv
for some matrix
H\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
. Motivated by the form of the Kalman gain itself, and equations (
6.16
) in particular, but
invoking a steady-state hypothesis on the dynamics, we seek
K
in the form

(9.17)

K=\widehat{C}H^{\top}\bigl(H\widehat{C}H^{\top}+\Gamma\bigr)^{-1},

where
\widehat{C}
is a time-independent state covariance.
See Subsection
6.3.3
for details of the
derivation of this relationship between
K
and
\widehat{C}.

In the original derivation of 3DVar,
\widehat{C}
itself is estimated and
K
is formed using (
9.17
). Estimation of
\widehat{C}
is achieved using data relating to
the underlying dynamics model (
9.1
a), and estimates of the covariance in forecasts made by this model. However, in the remainder of this subsection we adopt a different approach, aiming to determine
K
directly,
under Data Assumption
9.5
.

General
\Psi
and
h
: A Machine Learning Approach to 3DVar

We now move away from the assumption that
H
is linear and we consider general
\Psi
and
h
. We note that
v_{j}
arising from algorithm class (
9.16
) depends on the gain matrix:
v_{j}=v_{j}(K).
We use the loss function in (
9.11
)
to determine the optimal choice of
\theta
:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{J}(K)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathsf{d}\bigl(v_{j}(K),v^{\dagger}_{j}\bigr),

\displaystyle K^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{K\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}}{\mathsf{J}}^{J}(K).

Remark 9.12
.

In the learning frameworks of Chapter
4
we introduced the
use of transport maps to solve inverse problems. There we started from the population loss, defined as an expectation over a measure with Lebesgue density, and then noted that in practice we approximate this empirically.
Here, because the analog of the population loss is more complicated to write down, we work the other way around: we have started with empirical loss (
9.11
) defined by a time series of length
J
; and now proceed to derive the analog of a population
loss by letting
J\to\infty.
To this end, let us view (
9.1
), (
9.16
) as defining a coupled stochastic dynamical system for
(v^{\dagger}_{j},v_{j})
; this is obtained by substituting the expression for
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
into that for
v_{j+1}
.
The resulting stochastic dynamical system has the form

(9.18a)

\displaystyle v^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v^{\dagger}_{j})+\xi^{\dagger}_{j},

(9.18b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j})+K\Bigl(h\bigl(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}\bigr)-h\bigl(\Psi(v_{j})\bigr)+\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}\Bigr).

Recall the discussion of ergodicity in Section
14.1
, and in particular in Remark
14.3
. We assume that the dynamical system (
9.18
) is ergodic on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with invariant measure
\mu(dv^{\dagger},dv;K).
We may then view
{\mathsf{J}}^{J}(\cdot)
as approximation of the objective function
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)

{\mathsf{J}}(K)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{d}\bigl(v(K),v^{\dagger}\bigr)\,\mu(dv^{\dagger},dv;K).

By ergodicity, for any
K,

{\mathsf{J}}(K)=\lim_{J\to\infty}{\mathsf{J}}^{J}(K).

The objective function
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)
is the analog of the population loss in this setting.
This ergodicity observation also suggests that, rather than using one long trajectory, Data Assumption
9.5
could be modified to deploy a set of independently generated dynamics–data pairs from (
9.1
), rather than a single one.
∎

Linear
\Psi
and
h
: Expected Squared Error Loss Function for 3DVar

We now consider using an expected form of (
9.11
), with the deterministic scoring rule chosen to be the squared error, to determine the gain
K
. In this case, the expectation can be evaluated in closed form, leading to an implementable cost function.
We show that, in the setting of linear dynamics and observations, the learned gain
K

in the 3DVar algorithm (
9.16
) converges to the steady-state Kalman
gain as
J\to\infty
. To this end, consider the 3DVar algorithm (
9.16
)
with
\Psi(\cdot)=A\cdot
and
h(\cdot)=H\cdot
. Then (
9.1
),
(
9.16
) show that
(v^{\dagger}_{j},v_{j})
evolve according to the recursion

(9.19a)

\displaystyle v^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=Av^{\dagger}_{j}+\xi^{\dagger}_{j},

(9.19b)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=Av_{j},

(9.19c)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}

\displaystyle=(I-KH)\widehat{v}_{j+1}+K\bigl(Hv^{\dagger}_{j+1}+\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}\bigr).

Define the error covariances
\widehat{C}_{j}=\mathbb{E}\bigl[(v^{\dagger}_{j}-\widehat{v}_{j})\otimes(v^{\dagger}_{j}-\widehat{v}_{j})\bigr]
and
C_{j}=\mathbb{E}\bigl[(v^{\dagger}_{j}-v_{j})\otimes(v^{\dagger}_{j}-v_{j})\bigr]
, which we note are not the covariances of the forecast and filtering distributions. These covariances obey the recursions

(9.20a)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=AC_{j}A^{\top}+\Sigma,

(9.20b)

\displaystyle C_{j+1}

\displaystyle=(I-KH)\widehat{C}_{j+1}(I-KH)^{\top}+K\Gamma K^{\top}.

Identity (
9.20
a) holds because

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\widehat{v}_{j+1}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(v^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\widehat{v}_{j+1}\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl(Av^{\dagger}_{j}+\xi^{\dagger}_{j}-A{v}_{j}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(Av^{\dagger}_{j}+\xi^{\dagger}_{j}-A{v}_{j}\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl(A(v^{\dagger}_{j}-{v}_{j})\bigr)\otimes\bigl(A(v^{\dagger}_{j}-{v}_{j})\bigr)\Bigr]+\Sigma

\displaystyle=AC_{j}A^{\top}+\Sigma.

Identity (
9.20
b) holds because, from (
9.19
c),

v_{j+1}-v^{\dagger}_{j+1}=(I-KH)\bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}-v^{\dagger}_{j+1}\bigr)+K\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}.

In the following theorem, we show that the minimizer of an expected squared error objective function,
with
\theta:=K
, is the steady-state Kalman gain found from the Kalman filter in the limit
J\to\infty
.
We will use the preceding covariances and also use the definitions of the covariance
and gain arising from the Kalman filter itself, given in (
6.16
),
with a constant-in-time dynamics and observation operators
(A,H).

Here we recall the update formulae for the Kalman covariance
and the Kalman gain for notational convenience, introducing a new notation to distinguish from that in (
9.19
):

(9.21a)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}^{\sf{kalman}}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=AC^{\sf{kalman}}_{j}A^{\top}+\Sigma,

(9.21b)

\displaystyle K^{\sf{kalman}}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{\sf{kalman}}H^{\top}\bigl(H\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{\sf{kalman}}H^{\top}+\Gamma\bigr)^{-1},

(9.21c)

\displaystyle C^{\sf{kalman}}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=(I-K^{\sf{kalman}}_{j+1}H)\widehat{C}^{\sf{kalman}}_{j+1}.

Note the resemblance of the covariance update equations (
9.20
) to those of (
9.21
); in fact, (
9.20
b) reduces to (
9.21
c) if the gain
K
is chosen to be the Kalman gain (
9.21
b).

Theorem 9.13
.

Consider the 3DVar algorithm (
9.16
) with
\Psi(\cdot)=A\cdot
and
h(\cdot)=H\cdot
. Assume that the analysis covariance and gain of the Kalman filter, given by (
9.21
),
converge to their steady states in (
6.19
), (
6.20
):

C_{j}^{\sf{kalman}}\rightarrow C_{\infty},\qquad K_{j}^{\sf{kalman}}\rightarrow K_{\infty},\qquad j\rightarrow\infty.

For
K\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
define
v_{j}(K)
via the
recursion (
9.19
) and define

{\mathsf{J}}(K):=\limsup_{J\to\infty}\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[|v^{\dagger}_{j}-v_{j}(K)|^{2}|Y^{\dagger}_{j}].

Equivalently, defining
C_{j}=C_{j}(K)
by the recursion (
9.20
), we have a closed form expression for the expectation,

{\mathsf{J}}(K):=\limsup_{J\to\infty}\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathrm{Tr}\bigl(C_{j}(K)\bigr).

Then, the minimizer of
{\mathsf{J}}
is the steady-state Kalman
gain
in (
6.19c
), i.e.,

K_{\infty}\in\argmin_{K\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}}{\mathsf{J}}(K).

Proof.

Consider the following recursion for a state estimator
v_{j}
, using a time-varying gain
K_{j+1}
, taking the form

(9.22a)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}

\displaystyle=(I-K_{j+1}H)Av_{j}+K_{j+1}y^{\dagger}_{j+1},

(9.22b)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=AC_{j}A^{\top}+\Sigma,

(9.22c)

\displaystyle C_{j+1}

\displaystyle=(I-K_{j+1}H)\widehat{C}_{j+1}(I-K_{j+1}H)^{\top}+K_{j+1}\Gamma K_{j+1}^{\top}.

Next note that the update for the state in (
9.19
) and the covariance in (
9.20
) can be written in this form with

K_{j+1}=K.

We now use the fact that the Kalman filter provides the exact filtering distribution for this system. Therefore the Kalman filter mean
v_{j}=\mathbb{E}[v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j}]
, being the conditional expectation, provides the minimum mean-squared error estimate of the state at each step; see Theorem
1.17
. That is, let
v_{j}
be the sequence given by the Kalman filter mean in (
6.15
). Then, for any measurable function
z_{j}
of the observations
Y^{\dagger}_{j}
we have

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|v_{j}^{\dagger}-z_{j}|^{2}\,|\,Y^{\dagger}_{j}}}\right]\geq\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|v_{j}^{\dagger}-v_{j}|^{2}\,|\,Y^{\dagger}_{j}}}\right].

The right-hand side corresponds to the trace of the error covariance
C_{j}(K_{j}^{\sf{kalman}})
, thereby showing that the Kalman filter gain minimizes the trace of the covariance among all gains
K
; that is,

(9.23)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\bigl(C_{j+1}(K)\bigr)\geq\mathrm{Tr}\bigl(C_{j+1}(K^{\sf{kalman}}_{j+1})\bigr)

for all
j
. From the assumption that the Kalman filter covariance and gain converge to their steady states
C_{\infty}
and
K_{\infty}
, respectively, and the continuity of the trace operator, we have

\mathrm{Tr}\bigl(C_{j+1}(K^{\sf{kalman}}_{j+1})\bigr)\rightarrow\mathrm{Tr}(C_{\infty}),\qquad j\rightarrow\infty.

Therefore, by the Stolz–Cesàro-mean theorem (see bibliography), the average of the trace of the analysis covariances over the sequence also converges. That is,

(9.24)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathrm{Tr}\bigl(C_{j+1}(K^{\sf{kalman}}_{j+1})\bigr)\rightarrow\mathrm{Tr}(C_{\infty}).

Lastly, averaging (
9.23
) over
J
steps and taking the limit
J\rightarrow\infty
we have

(9.25)

\liminf_{J\to\infty}\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathrm{Tr}\bigl(C_{j}(K)\bigr)\geq\lim_{J\to\infty}\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathrm{Tr}\bigl(C_{j}(K_{j}^{\sf{kalman}})\bigr)=\mathrm{Tr}(C_{\infty}),

where we have used the convergence of the limit in (
9.24
). The inequality in (
9.25
) holds for all
K
and the lower bound is attained by
K=K_{\infty}.
From (
9.25
), we have
{\mathsf{J}}(K)\geq{\mathsf{J}}(K_{\infty})
, and hence the minimum value of the objective is attained with choice
K=K_{\infty}
.
∎

9.2.2
Learning the Gain in EnKF

The ensemble Kalman filter
was designed as a Monte Carlo method,
in which the ensemble is viewed as providing an
approximation of the filtering distribution. However it is often used as a state estimator, with the ensemble mean providing the estimate of the state.
This is the perspective we adopt in this subsection. We continue to work under
Data Assumption
9.5

and recall the EnKF algorithm (
6.42
). Rather than calculating
K_{j+1}
from empirical covariances as in (
6.38
), we instead try and learn dependence on the ensemble. To this end we modify (
6.42
) to read

(9.26a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j}^{(n)})+\xi^{(n)}_{j},\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(9.26b)

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=h(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)})+\eta^{(n)}_{j},\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(9.26c)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}+K_{j+1}\bigl(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)}\bigr),\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(9.26d)

\displaystyle K_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{k}\bigl(\{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(\ell)},\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(\ell)}\}_{\ell=1}^{N};\theta\bigr),

where
\xi_{j}^{(n)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
,
\eta_{j+1}^{(n)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Gamma)

are independent sequences of i.i.d. random vectors with respect to both
j
and
n
, and
the two sequences themselves are independent of one another.
This is then a specific subclass of the algorithm class defined in (
9.3
).
It is useful to define a state estimator by taking the mean of
the ensemble at time
j
as defined in (
9.5
). We may then use
the loss function in (
9.11
) to determine
\theta,

with
v_{j}(\theta)
given by the ensemble mean in (
9.5
).

As explained in the text preceding (
9.8
) it is desirable that
\mathsf{k}(\cdot\,;\theta)
be invariant with respect to permutation of the ensemble; this may be achieved by viewing the dependence on
the ensemble as being defined through allowing dependence only on the empirical measure (
9.7
). The following example highlights a specific way of achieving this.

Example 9.14
.

It may be of interest to replace (
9.26d
) by

K_{j+1}=\mathsf{k}\bigl(\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{vh},\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{yy};\theta\bigr),

where the covariance matrices are as defined in (
6.38
) and

\mathsf{k}(\cdot\,;\theta)
is a parameterized family of gain functions to be learned by optimizing over
\theta;
this automatically builds in the
desired permutation invariance from Remark
9.1
. Function

\mathsf{k}(\cdot;\theta)
may be parameterized, for example, as a neural network.
Recalling definition (
6.43
) of the innovation,
it may also be of interest to replace
(
9.26
c,
9.26
d) by an update of the form

\displaystyle v_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{k}\bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},\widehat{m}_{j+1},i_{j+1}^{(n)},\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{vh},\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{yy},\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{vv};\theta\bigr),\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

\displaystyle i_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)},\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

where

\widehat{m}_{j+1}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}

is the mean of the predicted forecast ensemble.

Recall from Remark
6.13
that if
h
is linear then the two covariances

\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{vh},\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{yy}
can be expressed in terms of

\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{vv}
and
H
; thus, in this case we might seek to learn the
gain in the form
\mathsf{k}\bigl(\widehat{C}_{j+1}^{vv},H;\theta\bigr).

The reader will be able to suggest many other variants on the preceding formulations of a learning problem for an EnKF-like data assimilation algorithm. One such variant is described in Subsection
9.4.1
, where the learning
objective is to match the filtering distribution.
∎

9.2.3
Learning Localization and Inflation in EnKF

Inflation and localization, discussed in Subsection
6.3.6
, are essential for the performance of the EnKF, as discussed in Remark
6.12
. Learning can be used to determine appropriate parameters for these features of the EnKF. Again we continue to work under Data Assumption
9.5
.

Recall that the multiplicative approach to inflation given by (
6.45
) has the form

\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}\to\widehat{m}_{j+1}+\alpha(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}-\widehat{m}_{j+1}),

where
\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}
are predicted states in the ensemble and
\widehat{m}_{j+1}
is their mean. Hadamard product covariance localization
given by (
6.46
), (
6.47
) has the form

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{j+1}

\displaystyle\to L\circ\widehat{C}_{j+1},

\displaystyle(L)_{ab}

\displaystyle=e^{-\mathsf{d}(a,b)^{2}/\ell^{2}},

where
\circ
denotes the Hadamard product,
a,b\in\{1,\dots,d\}
represent variable indices, and
\mathsf{d}(a,b)
measures the distance between the variables corresponding to the indices. If both inflation and localization are to be learned
simultaneously, then the inflation parameter
\alpha
and localization radius
\ell
can be considered to form parameter
\theta:=(\alpha,\ell)
. We may again use
the loss function in (
9.11
) to determine
\theta,

with
v_{j}(\theta)
given by the ensemble mean in (
9.5
).

Remark 9.15
.

It may be of interest to separately learn either inflation or localization, and
generalization to do this is straightforward. Furthermore,
other ways of parameterizing localization could also be considered, such as taking
\theta=L
and learning the entire matrix, or parameterizing
L(\theta)
with a given structure different from the one given above. Alternatives to Hadamard product localization can also be considered, such as ones that learn a nonlinear map
\mathfrak{L}(\cdot\,;\theta)
that localizes the gain,
K_{j}\to\mathfrak{L}(K_{j};\theta).

∎

9.2.4
Optimal Particle Filter

Here we work in the setting of the linear observation operator (
6.62
),
leading to the following state–observation dynamics model, repeated here for convenience:

\displaystyle v^{\dagger}_{j=1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v^{\dagger}_{j})+\xi^{\dagger}_{j},

\displaystyle y^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=Hv^{\dagger}_{j+1}+\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}\,.

We again make the same Gaussian and independence
assumptions on the initialization and noise as
are made in equations (
6.1
),
(
6.2
) and Assumption
6.2
.
Recall that the optimal particle filter, from Subsection
6.3.8
,
works by proposing
particles through an ensemble of equally weighted noisy 3DVar estimators and then reweighting them:
see equation (
6.64
). Here, recognizing that reweighting often leads to particle
collapse (see citations in Section
6.8
),
we instead seek to learn a desirable combination of the ensemble of 3DVars:

(9.27a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=(I-KH)\Psi(v_{j}^{(n)})+Ky^{\dagger}_{j+1}+\zeta_{j+1}^{(n)},\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(9.27b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}+\mathsf{k}\Bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},y^{\dagger}_{j+1},i_{j+1}^{(n)},\{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(\ell)}\}_{\ell=1}^{N};\theta\Bigr),\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(9.27c)

\displaystyle i_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-H\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},\quad n=1,\ldots,N.

Thus the innovation is defined analogously to
(
6.43
), measuring how closely the predicted state
matches the data.
Random variables
\zeta_{j+1}^{(n)}
are i.i.d. in
j
and
n
and distributed according to
\mathcal{N}(0,C),
where, as in (
6.63
):

(9.28)

\displaystyle C=(I-KH)\Sigma,\quad K=\Sigma H^{\top}S^{-1},\quad S=H\Sigma H^{\top}+\Gamma.

Again, as explained in Remark
9.1
, it is desirable that
\mathsf{k}(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},y^{\dagger}_{j+1},i_{j+1}^{(n)},\cdot\,;\theta)

is invariant with respect to permutation of the ensemble. The proposed form
of the algorithm is a generalization of (
9.3
).

We continue to work under Data Assumption
9.5
.
To emphasize dependence of the algorithm on the parameters
\theta
to be learned,
we again write
v_{j}^{(n)}(\theta)
and consider the state estimator in (
9.5
). We may then determine
\theta
from (
9.11
).

Remark 9.16
.

As in the context of the EnKF, other forms of learning problems may be
postulated; see Example
9.14
. One generalization specific
to this optimal particle filter–based approach
relates to the fact that it may be desirable to learn
the fixed gain
K
appearing in (
9.27
a) alongside
\theta
,
rather than fix
K
according to (
9.28
).
We also note that the update step in (
9.27
b) contains multiple
redundancies and could be simplified to

v_{j+1}^{(n)}=\mathsf{k}\Bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},i_{j+1}^{(n)},\{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(\ell)}\}_{\ell=1}^{N};\theta\Bigr),\quad n=1,\ldots,N.

∎

9.2.5
Ergodicity of Ensemble and Particle Filters

In Remark
9.12
we discussed how our proposed learning framework
for generalizations of 3DVar can be viewed in terms of ergodicity of
the underlying filter.
In the ensemble and particle filters described in preceding subsections,
the notion of ergodicity is also relevant and it can be
extended to these interacting particle systems; we do not write down
the details as it is notationally heavy to do so and as the details are
not exploited in this book.
As in Remark
9.12
, ergodicity may be used to define the large
J

limit of the optimization problem (
9.11
).
Beyond the state estimation setting considered in this section, the idea of ergodicity is also relevant to define the large
J

limit of the optimization problem (
9.29
)
that we define in the next section for
probabilistic estimation.

9.3
Probabilistic Estimation: Learning Objectives

In Section
9.1
we introduced learning objectives for state
estimation;
this is followed in Section
9.2
in which we
study algorithms learned according to state estimation objectives.
