In this section we introduce probabilistic learning objectives,
aimed at probabilistic estimation;
in the following Section
9.4
, we study
algorithms learned to perform well at probabilistic estimation.

Thus our aim in this section is to introduce learning objectives that
may be used to optimize a specified class of algorithms
with respect to their ability to approximate
the filtering distribution.
In Subsection
9.3.1
we set out a desirable learning objective, but
highlight the fact that implementation is difficult precisely because the
true filtering distribution is not known.
Subsection
9.3.2
proposes using components of the
bootstrap particle filter to
train an equal weights particle filter.
In Subsection
9.3.3
we describe the same idea, but in the context of
the optimal particle filter.
In Subsection
9.3.4
we provide links to others parts of the book where
ideas from variational inference
are used to learn approximate filters from probabilistic learning objectives.

The learning objectives we consider for probabilistic estimation are based on various “distance-like” ways to quantify closeness between
probability distributions studied in Chapter
11
. In our filtering context, it is essential to consider metrics, divergences and scoring rules that are implementable given only samples, since pointwise evaluation of the underlying density is often infeasible. We refer to Remark
11.78
for a discussion of choices of distance-like measures with this attribute.

9.3.1
Matching the Filtering Distribution

Let
\mathsf{D}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a divergence, as defined in Section
11.2
.
A desirable objective function for matching the approximate filtering distribution
\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}(\theta)
to the true filtering distribution
\pi_{j}

defined in Subsection
6.2.1
,
over
j\in\{1,\ldots,J\},
is

(9.29a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{J}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathsf{D}(\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}(\theta)\|\pi_{j}),

(9.29b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}^{J}(\theta).

However, implementation of such an objective is difficult because the
true filtering distribution
\pi_{j}
cannot be constructed explicitly,
except in simple cases such as the linear Gaussian setting, leading
to the Kalman filter;
thus creation of training data
\{y^{\dagger}_{j},\pi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
to enable
solution of the minimization problem (
9.29
) is
difficult. In contrast, when state estimation is
the goal of the learning problem, as detailed in Subsection
9.1.1
,
it is possible to simulate training data
\{v^{\dagger}_{j},y^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}

from the stochastic dynamics model, as in Data Assumption
9.5
.

A variety of approaches exist to address the difficulty of not having
training data for probabilistic estimation.
We detail these in the next three subsections. The first two build on the use
of ideas underlying the bootstrap and optimal particle filters;
the third uses ideas from variational inference.
Furthermore we note that, in the context of amortization, probabilistic scoring rules may be
used, circumventing the need to be able to simulate
from the filtering distribution; see Remark
9.10
and
Section
10.3
.

Remark 9.17
.

An essential difficulty to be confronted when learning approximate filters using a probabilistic
learning objective is that filtering involves repeated solution
of an inverse problem, at each step
j
; the operator
\mathsf{A}_{j}

appearing in (
6.5
) is an operator representing the map from prior

\mathbb{P}(v_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})
to posterior

\mathbb{P}(v_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j},y^{\dagger}_{j+1}).

The prior used to solve this inverse problem does
not have a closed form and differs at each step
j
of the
filtering procedure. The issue of having a prior
only known through samples is addressed for inverse problems
in Subsection
4.2.2
, but the assumption there is that only
one prior is considered, and exact samples from it are known. Neither
assumption applies in the filtering setting.
∎

9.3.2
Matching the Filtering Distribution: Transport

The aim of the methodology proposed in this subsection is to learn equal weights
filters that benefit from both the robustness of methods with
equal weights and the accuracy of the bootstrap and optimal particle filters; the discussion
in Subsection
6.3.9
serves as motivation.

We consider learning parameter
\theta
appearing in an
equal weights ensemble filter
algorithm of the form (
9.10
).
In what follows we employ the empirical measures
\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}
and

\widehat{\pi}^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j+1}
which are defined in (
9.4
) and
(
9.6
) respectively.
Recall that the filtering update may be factorized
as in (
6.48
) into composition of prediction and
analysis steps:

\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}={\mathsf{P}}\pi_{j},\,\,\,\,\,\pi_{j+1}={\mathsf{A}}_{j}(\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}).

The step (
9.10
a) generates
\widehat{\pi}^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j+1}\approx{\mathsf{P}}\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}
,
approximating the prediction step
{\mathsf{P}}
. The step (
9.10
b) maps

\widehat{\pi}^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j+1}
to
\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j+1}
, approximating the analysis step
{\mathsf{A}}_{j}
.
Using transport, as in the step (
9.10
b), to
approximate Bayesian inversion when the prior is only known through
samples is the subject of Section
4.3
; we use
those ideas, now in the context of data assimilation.

We work under the following data assumption:

Data Assumption 9.18
.

We are given data
\{y^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
generated through a realization of (
9.1
); furthermore we are able to evaluate
\mathsf{l}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|\cdot\,).

For compactness of notation we write the step (
9.10
b) as

(9.30)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=T_{j}\Bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)};y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\theta\Bigr),\quad n=1,\ldots,N.

We have removed explicit dependence on the
ensemble
\{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(\ell)}\}_{\ell=1}^{N}
and use subscript
j
on
T
to indicate it.
To define the optimization problem for
\theta
we proceed as follows.
The empirical measure
\widehat{\pi}^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j+1}
given in (
9.6
) may itself be reweighted by likelihood information in order to obtain an
approximation
2
2

2

\mathsf{walg}
for “weighted algorithm”.

\pi^{\mathrm{walg}}_{j+1}
of the analysis distribution at time
j+1
:

(9.31)

\pi^{\mathrm{walg}}_{j+1}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}w_{j+1}^{(n)}\delta_{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}},\quad\ell_{j+1}^{(n)}=\mathsf{l}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}),\quad w_{j+1}^{(n)}=\frac{\ell_{j+1}^{(n)}}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell_{j+1}^{(i)}},\quad

where the likelihood weights are normalized to sum to one so that
\pi^{\mathrm{walg}}_{j+1}
is a probability measure. Now recall the energy distance

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm E}}}^{2}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

introduced in Definition
11.27
.
Using construction (
9.31
) we may estimate an optimal
\theta^{\star}
that minimizes the distance at each analysis step, averaged over the time series:

(9.32a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm E}}}^{2}\Bigl(T_{j}(\cdot\,;y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\theta)_{\sharp}\widehat{\pi}^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j+1}(\theta),\pi^{\mathrm{walg}}_{j+1}(\theta)\Bigr),

(9.32b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta).

This is a time-averaged version of the loss function introduced, for the solution of
inverse problems, in Section
4.3
.
After finding
\theta^{\star}
, and hence
T_{j}(\cdot\,;y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\theta^{\star})
,
we can evaluate the map at samples from the prediction to generate analysis samples.

The resulting learning approach implied by the objective (
9.32
)
may be viewed as a form of
unsupervised learning
–
we do not use explicit information
from the prior. As a result the performance can be poor.
The proposed learning objective may have most value
when employed in conjunction with other learning objectives,
that anchor the particles to the true signal, and hence anchor
the prior particle distribution encountered at each step of
the filtering process to be close to the truth.
To this end we combine Data Assumptions

9.5
and
9.18
to obtain

Data Assumption 9.19
.

We are given data
\{v^{\dagger}_{j},y^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
generated through a realization of (
9.1
); furthermore we are able to evaluate
\mathsf{l}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|\cdot\,).

We may then combine the objectives (
9.32
)
and (
9.11
) to obtain, for
\lambda\in(0,1)
,
the optimization problem

(9.33a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\lambda\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm E}}}^{2}\Bigl(T_{j}(\cdot;y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\theta)_{\sharp}\widehat{\pi}^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j+1}(\theta),\pi^{\mathrm{walg}}_{j+1}(\theta)\Bigr)+(1-\lambda)\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathsf{d}\bigl(v_{j}(\theta),v^{\dagger}_{j}\bigr),

(9.33b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta).

Remark 9.20
.

By resampling
\pi^{\mathrm{walg}}_{j+1}
we may obtain approximate
i.i.d. samples
\{\mathfrak{v}_{j+1}^{(m)}\}_{m=1}^{M}
from the analysis
distribution
\pi_{j+1}
at time
j+1
arising from the analysis step in (
6.4
).
We then have the alternate approximation of the analysis distribution given by

(9.34)

\pi^{\mathrm{ralg}}_{j+1}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\delta_{{\mathfrak{v}}_{j+1}^{(m)}}.

Using
\pi^{\mathrm{ralg}}_{j+1}
in (
9.34
) instead of
\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j+1}
in (
9.32
), we may estimate an optimal
\theta^{\star}
by solving the following problem:

(9.35a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{N,M}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm E}}}^{2}\Bigl(T_{j}(\cdot\,;y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\theta)_{\sharp}\widehat{\pi}^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j+1}(\theta),\pi^{\mathrm{ralg}}_{j+1}(\theta)\Bigr),

(9.35b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}^{N,M}(\theta).

Methods for numerically approximating the objective function through sampling and resampling
are outlined in Section
4.3
.
We may also anchor this objective function to the true signal, similar to
(
9.33
).
∎

9.3.3
Matching the Filtering Distribution: Generalized Transport

Recall the OPF from
Subsection
6.3.8
. In this subsection we generalize
the transport ideas from the previous subsection to
incorporate the OPF proposal, rather than the
BPF proposal.
As in the previous subsection, our main motivation is
avoiding the problem of weight collapse described in
Subsection
6.3.9
. Recall, from (
6.57
),
that the two steps in the OPF factorization may be written as

(9.36a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{A}_{j}^{\textrm{OPF}}(\pi_{j}),\quad\pi_{j+1}=\mathsf{P}_{j}^{\textrm{OPF}}\mathfrak{p}_{j+1},

(9.36b)

\displaystyle\pi_{j}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j}),\quad\mathfrak{p}_{j+1}:=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j+1}).

Importantly for the benefits of the method over the BPF,
both steps depend on the data
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
.

In the preceding subsection we showed how to make an equal weights
particle filter by
defining a transport that is close to the analysis step in the
BPF.
We now consider an analogous approach, based on proposing particles
using the optimal particle filter. Let

(9.37)

\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{v_{j}^{(n)}}

be an approximation of the true filtering distribution.
Note that in Subsection
6.3.8
the approximation is
different, being based on a non-equally weighted set of
points
\{\widehat{v}_{j}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
and given in (
6.58
) as:

(9.38)

\pi^{\mathrm{OPF}}_{j}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}w_{j}^{(n)}\delta_{\widehat{v}_{j}^{(n)}}.

This is sampled, in the step (
6.59
a) of the
OPF algorithm from Subsection
6.3.8
,
to obtain equally weighted points
\{v_{j}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
; these
particles could have been used to state an approximate
filtering distribution with form (
9.37
).

We now describe learnable particle evolutions for the
\{v_{j}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
.
Our use of the variables with and without hats differs from the
useage in our initial exposition of the OPF
contained in Subsection
6.3.8
. This is because, in that context,
it is possible to commute the order in which mappings

\mathsf{A}_{j}^{\textrm{OPF}}
and
\mathsf{P}_{j}^{\textrm{OPF}}

are undertaken; this is because application of
\mathsf{A}_{j}^{\textrm{OPF}}

corresponds, in Subsection
6.3.8
, to a simple reweighting. Here we
replace it by a transport map that must be executed before
application of
\mathsf{P}_{j}^{\textrm{OPF}}
.
Recall matrices
K
,
S
, and
C
defined in (
6.63
) and consider the following algorithm class: for
n=1,\ldots,N
,

(9.39a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=T_{j}\Bigl(v_{j}^{(n)};y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\theta\Bigr),

(9.39b)

\displaystyle{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=(I-KH)\Psi(\widehat{v}_{j}^{(n)})+Ky^{\dagger}_{j+1}+\zeta_{j+1}^{(n)},

where
\zeta_{j+1}^{(n)}~\text{i.i.d.}\thinspace\mathcal{N}(0,C)
.
Step (
9.39
a) is a particle, transport-based, approximation of the action of
\mathsf{A}_{j}^{\textrm{OPF}}
, resulting in a particle approximation
of
\mathfrak{p}_{j+1};
step (
9.39
b) gives samples from an approximation
of
{\mathsf{P}}_{j}^{\textrm{OPF}}\mathfrak{p}_{j+1}.

We view the resulting method as an equal weights ensemble filter and thus obtain the approximate filtering distribution at time
j+1
from formula (
9.38
) with
j\mapsto j+1:

\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j+1}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{v_{j+1}^{(n)}}.

We note that

T_{j}(\cdot\,;y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\theta)_{\sharp}\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}\approx\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{walg}}_{j+1}:=\mathsf{A}_{j}^{\textrm{OPF}}(\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}).

On the other hand
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{walg}}_{j+1}
may be obtained exactly
by employing the OPF weightings (
6.59
b), resulting in the
linear observations setting in (
6.64
):

(9.40)

\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{walg}}_{j+1}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}w_{j+1}^{(n)}\delta_{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}},\quad\ell_{j+1}^{(n)}=\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{2}|y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-H\Psi(v_{j}^{(n)})|_{S}^{2}}}\right),\quad w_{j+1}^{(n)}=\frac{\ell_{j+1}^{(n)}}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell_{j+1}^{(i)}}.

The methods of the preceding subsection may now be generalized,
leading to the following optimization problem, defined for any

\lambda\in(0,1):

(9.41a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\lambda\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm E}}}^{2}\Bigl(T_{j}(\cdot;y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\theta)_{\sharp}\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j}(\theta),\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{walg}}_{j+1}(\theta)\Bigr)+(1-\lambda)\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathsf{d}\bigl(v_{j}(\theta),v^{\dagger}_{j}\bigr).

(9.41b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta).

9.3.4
Matching the Filtering Distribution: Variational Bayes

In Sections
7.2
and
7.3
we demonstrate how variational Bayes may be used to design objective functions from which to learn approximate filters that are defined implicitly by the action of transports.
The reader is encouraged to study the resulting learning objectives implied by these approaches.
We include an example, based on the ideas in Section
7.2
, in
Subsection
9.4.3
.

9.4
Probabilistic Estimation: Algorithms

In this section we provide explicit instances of the general ideas developed in the
preceding section. Subsection
9.4.1
contains a specific example of the
transport methodology defined in Subsection
9.3.2
.
In Subsection
9.4.2
we include a specific example of the
generalized transport methodology defined in Subsection
9.3.3
.
Subsection
9.4.3
is devoted to learning the analysis map,
in the ensemble setting, using variational inference as outlined in Subsection
9.3.4
.

9.4.1
Transport

We describe an explicit example of the methodology introduced in Subsection
9.3.2
.
In particular we describe a specific class of equal weight particle
filters.
However, rather than using (
9.10
) as we did for simplicity in Subsection
9.3.2
,
we use the slightly more general form (
9.9
). Within this class we consider an algorithm of the form,
for
n=1,\ldots,N
,

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j}^{(n)})+\xi^{(n)}_{j},

\displaystyle v_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}+K_{j+1}\bigl(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\eta_{j+1}^{(n)}-h(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)})\bigr),

\displaystyle K_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{k}\Bigl(\{\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(\ell)},h\bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}\bigr)\}_{\ell=1}^{N},\Gamma;\theta\Bigr).

The noise entering these particle updates satisfy Assumption
6.11
.
We generalize the form of Kalman gain
K_{j+1}

used in the EnKF
and given by (
6.38
).
To this end we assume the following form for
\mathsf{k}
:

(9.42)

\mathsf{k}=C_{\theta,j+1}^{(1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(C_{\theta,j+1}^{(2)}+\Gamma}}\right)^{-1}.

Here
C_{\theta,j+1}^{(1)}
and
C_{\theta,j+1}^{(2)}
are modified versions of
\widehat{C}^{vh}_{j+1}
and
\widehat{C}^{hh}_{j+1}
from (
6.38
), with modifications parameterized by
\theta.

Specifically we have, recalling (
9.6
),

(9.43a)

\displaystyle C_{\theta,j+1}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\widehat{v}\sim{\widehat{\pi}^{\mathrm{alg}}}_{j+1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\widehat{v}-\widehat{m}+\widehat{w}_{\theta}}}\right)\otimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(h(\widehat{v})-\widehat{h}+\widehat{z}_{\theta}}}\right)}}\right],\quad\widehat{m}=\mathbb{E}^{\widehat{v}\sim{\widehat{\pi}^{\mathrm{alg}}}_{j+1}}\bigl(\widehat{v}\bigr),

(9.43b)

\displaystyle C_{\theta,j+1}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\widehat{v}\sim{\widehat{\pi}^{\mathrm{alg}}}_{j+1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(h(\widehat{v})-\widehat{h}+\widehat{z}_{\theta}}}\right)\otimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(h(\widehat{v})-\widehat{h}+\widehat{z}_{\theta}}}\right)}}\right],\quad\widehat{h}=\mathbb{E}^{\widehat{v}\sim{\widehat{\pi}^{\mathrm{alg}}}_{j+1}}\bigl(h(\widehat{v})\bigr).

Here
\widehat{w}_{\theta}
and
\widehat{z}_{\theta}
are learnable functions, parameterized by
\theta
, that correct the basic form of the EnKF. They are assumed to depend on the state
\widehat{v}
, observation
h(\widehat{v})
, true observation
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
and the distribution
{\widehat{\pi}^{\mathrm{alg}}}_{j+1}
.

9.4.2
Generalized Transport

Here we consider application of the ideas proposed in Subsection
9.3.3
, exploiting the
OPF. We work in the setting of linear observation operator
where the method is tractable, thus considering the data assimilation problem defined
by (
6.62
). Recall the Kalman gain
K
and covariances
C
,
S

defined by (
6.63
).

We study a particular subclass of the equal weights particle filter (
9.39
) and consider particles evolving according to, for
n=1,\ldots,N
,

(9.44a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=v_{j}^{(n)}+K_{j}\Bigl(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\varsigma_{j+1}^{(n)}-H\Psi\bigl(v_{j}^{(n)}\bigr)\Bigr),

(9.44b)

\displaystyle K_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{k}\Bigl(\{v_{j}^{(\ell)},\Psi\bigl(v_{j}^{(\ell)}\bigr)\}_{\ell=1}^{N},H,S;\theta\Bigr),

(9.44c)

\displaystyle{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=(I-KH)\Psi(\widehat{v}_{j}^{(n)})+Ky^{\dagger}_{j+1}+\zeta_{j+1}^{(n)},

where
\zeta_{j+1}^{(n)},\varsigma_{j+1}^{(n)}
are distributed
i.i.d.
respectively,
according to
\mathcal{N}(0,C)
and
\mathcal{N}(0,S)
, in
j
and
n
, analogous to Assumption
6.11
.
Remark
9.1
concerning permutation invariance is also relevant here.

9.4.3
Variational Bayes

In this section we present a formulation for learning parameters based on the variational inference objectives introduced in Section
7.2
. To do so, we consider a class of variational approximations defined by

q_{j+1}(\theta)=T_{j}(\cdot;y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\theta)_{\sharp}\mathsf{P}q_{j}(\theta),\quad q_{0}=\pi_{0}.

As in Subsection
9.3.2
we use symbol
T_{j}
to denote dependence of the transport on the predicted
ensemble. And, in particular, by building in permutation invariance this may be viewed as dependence on

\widehat{\pi}^{\mathrm{alg}}_{j+1}
defined in (
9.6
).

If
q_{j}
is an empirical measure

q_{j}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{v_{j}^{(n)}},

then
q_{j+1}
is also an empirical measure whose samples are defined by first sampling from the forecast model, followed by evaluating the transport map. That is,

\displaystyle q_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{v_{j+1}^{(n)}},

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}=\Psi(v_{j}^{(n)})+\xi_{j}^{(n)},

\displaystyle v_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=T_{j}(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)};y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\theta),

\displaystyle v_{0}^{(n)}\sim\pi_{0}.

Our goal is to identify an element of this class of measures
q_{j}
, by selecting
\theta
that approximates the filtering distribution
\pi_{j}
at step
j
of filtering using the variational Bayes objective in (
7.7
). Recall the likelihood
\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v^{\dagger}_{j+1})
and
the objective function (
7.7
):

{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(\theta)=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q_{j+1}(\theta)\|{\mathsf{P}}q_{j}(\theta))-\mathbb{E}^{v^{\dagger}_{j+1}\sim q_{j+1}(\theta)}[\log\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v^{\dagger}_{j+1})].

Since we have access to the likelihood in the filtering setting, the second term in the preceding
objective can be directly evaluated for the empirical measure to obtain

(9.45)

-\mathbb{E}^{v^{\dagger}_{j+1}\sim q_{j+1}(\theta)}[\log\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v^{\dagger}_{j+1})]=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\log\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|v^{(n)}_{j+1}).

To differentiate
{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(\theta)
with respect to
\theta
the reparameterization trick, defined in Subsection
15.7.3
,
can be used to obtain the gradient with respect to
\theta
.
However, the first term is problematic since one cannot evaluate the KL divergence between two empirical measures. To
address this issue we make an approximation by first projecting into the space of Gaussian measures. Using the Gaussian projection
\mathsf{G}
introduced in Remark
2.13
, we make the replacement

(9.46)

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q_{j+1}(\theta)\|\mathsf{P}q_{j}(\theta))\mapsto\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\Bigl(\mathsf{G}(q_{j+1}(\theta))\|\mathsf{G}\bigl(\mathsf{P}q_{j}(\theta)\bigr)\Bigr),

and then use the formula for the KL divergence between two Gaussian measures, (
11.18
).
One can then either minimize the sum over
j
of the objectives
{\mathsf{J}}_{j}
, as in (
7.11
), or minimize each
{\mathsf{J}}_{j}
individually, as in (
7.8
).

Remark 9.21
.

The substitution (
9.46
) does not lead to a viable method if
N<d
, since the covariance matrix of the resulting Gaussian will be rank-deficient, leading to a degenerate distribution for which the KL divergence cannot be computed. The covariance matrix can be modified to be full rank by a technique such as localization; see Subsection
6.3.6
. asWe may also wish to learn localization, as discussed in
Subsection
9.2.3
.
∎

Remark 9.22
.

Using the invariance of the KL divergence under invertible transformations, it can be shown that an alternative to (
9.46
) can be formulated that uses the Gaussian projection
\mathsf{G}
only once rather than twice; see the bibliography.
∎

9.5
Bibliography

This chapter presents various methodologies for learning algorithms to solve both the
state and probabilistic estimation
problems in data assimilation.
We adopt a unifying framework based on transport maps. We show how several learning problems for classical algorithms, such as learning the gain in 3DVar, can be viewed through the
lens of machine learning; and we generalize the classical EnKF algorithms to create new equal weights particle filters. Whilst some of the proposed algorithms have already been implemented and tested, demonstrating favourable behaviour, not all of the proposed methods have been implemented. It is thus of interest to examine the performance of variants proposed here, such as the methods in Section
9.3
, which have not yet been tested. In this section, we provide references to the current state-of-the-art.

Learning approaches for ensemble filtering and smoothing based on transportation of measure have shown recent promise in generalizing classic Kalman approaches and reducing the error. The paper
[
446
]
introduces the idea of learning transports
to build prior-to-posterior maps, while
[
12
]
seeks optimal transport maps by solving adversarial learning problems. A related framework known as Gaussian anamorphosis seeks invertible transformations of the state and observations to a space where Kalman algorithms apply. See
[
216
]
for an approach based on nonlinear diagonal transformations and a generalization that uses invertible neural networks in
[
125
]
. Other nonlinear generalizations of ensemble Kalman filtering methods include
[
241
,
22
,
310
]
. A nonlinear filter that is derived using variational inference, based on transports that lie in reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (see Example
11.18
) is
[
393
]
. Approaches based on diffusion models are provided in
[
46
,
242
]
. An operator learning perspective on learning smoothers is provided in
[
93
]
.

Approaches to parameterize and learn the gain to minimize state estimation error has been studied in various settings. 3DVar gain learning was considered in
[
239
,
328
]
, and a lower-dimensional parameterization was considered in
[
240
]
; geophysical applications motivated this work.
The form of 3DVar, based on estimation of
\widehat{C}
and use of (
6.16a
) to form the
gain, originated in numerical weather prediction
[
323
,
322
]
. It was proven in
[
239
,
328
]
that the fixed gain which minimizes the expected error with respect to observations, in the asymptotic limit, is the steady-state Kalman gain. However, the cost functions considered were different and
[
239
]
required assumptions on the rank of
H
. Learning the gain was also considered in
[
312
]
with nonlinear dynamics.
For the statement and proof of the Stolz–Cesàro theorem, used in the proof of
Theorem
9.13
, see
[
355
, Theorem 1.23]
.

The learning of a neural network analysis step by minimizing a state estimation loss is considered in
[
338
]
. The analysis step there is EnKF-like in the sense that the ensemble members interact during the analysis step only through the ensemble mean and covariance. The learning of an analysis step is also considered in
[
72
]
. Permutation-invariant neural networks were used for postprocessing of an ensemble in
[
247
]
, and for learning equal weight ensemble filters in
[
37
]
. The architecture described in Subsection
9.4.1

is closely to that proposed in
[
37
]
. The paper

[
37
]
considers a general framework for learning, based on minimizing state estimation error, and generalizing the ensemble Kalman filter; the set transformer is used to
encode ensembles of arbitrary size with a single set of learned parameters, and fine-tuning is
used for localization and inflation parameters which are
dependent on ensemble size.
The issue of estimating optimism and out-of-sample performance of DA algorithms is discussed in
[
85
,
328
]
.
Various approaches to learning inflation are discussed in
[
21
,
348
]
. Learning localization by minimizing the squared Euclidean distance between the analysis and the true trajectory is considered in
[
499
]
, while a similar approach with a different scoring rule is considered in
[
350
]
. Other approaches to learning localization are discussed in
[
389
,
123
,
269
,
391
,
488
]
.
Emulating the analysis step of a Kalman filter using recurrent neural networks is considered in
[
230
]
.
An ensemble estimator for the energy score is introduced in
[
206
]
.

Several works have proposed to learn the filtering distribution directly without using knowledge of the model dynamics
[
84
,
81
,
416
]
. This is sometimes referred to as “end-to-end” learning. This has also been considered for state estimation in
[
476
,
152
,
16
,
93
]
.

The methodology presented in Subsection
9.4.3
for learning ensemble filters using variational inference is introduced in
[
38
]
. The reformulation of the objective function that avoids one of the Gaussian projections, mentioned in Remark
9.22
, is discussed therein.

Chapter 10
Data Dependence of Filters and Smoothers

In this chapter we use learning to solve the filtering and smoothing problems introduced in Chapter
6
. While we also considered learning filters and smoothers in Chapters
7
and
9
,
the key distinguishing concept in this chapter is
amortization
: the idea that, if we learn dependence of an algorithm on the observations, it may be re-used multiple times and hence the initial cost of training
is amortized. The success of such an approach depends on the ability of the proposed method to learn observation dependence that generalizes well.

Recall that the idea of learning based on
multiple instances of observational data, amortization, is introduced in earlier chapters. In Sections
5.1
–
5.4
of Chapter
5
we introduced amortization to approximate observed data dependence in the MAP estimator, the posterior distribution, and the likelihood function; we also used related data to learn regularizers in the context of inverse problems. Furthermore, we note that in Chapter
9
we have also learned models that take different observation sequences as input; there we use long time-trajectories and ergodicity, coupled with inductive bias in the form of the learned filters, to address the issue of learning dependence on different observation sequences. In this chapter our approach is more akin to
Chapter
5
, using multiple data sets, rather than one long trajectory and the appeal to ergodicity. We focus on learning observational data dependence for filtering and smoothing algorithms.

We consider both state estimation: finding a representative sequence of states from the observations and knowledge of the model; and probabilistic estimation: approximating the posterior distributions for sequences of states given observations.
The material is organized as follows. Section
10.1
is devoted to state estimation, and to learning observational data dependence
in 3DVar and 4DVar-type algorithms, as well as learning conditional mean estimators. In Section
10.2
we study
variational approaches from Chapter
7
, showing how to introduce amortization in both filtering and smoothing approaches. In Section
10.3
we introduce a learning paradigm for amortized data assimilation problems, based on strictly proper scoring rules. Section
10.4
is devoted to transport-based methods, generalizing those of Chapter
9
to the amortized setting; transport methodology is presented for learning ensemble filters by employing objectives based on strictly proper scoring rules or maximum likelihood. We also include a concrete example of learning transports for smoothing, again employing scoring rules. A key distinction between Section
10.4
and the earlier
Sections
10.1
–
10.3
is that the former develops online methods, whilst the other sections
learn offline. The chapter concludes with bibliographic remarks in Section
10.5
.
Throughout, we use the notational conventions summarized in Subsection
6.1.1
and in Remark
7.1
; in particular, we write

V^{\dagger}:=\{v^{\dagger}_{0},\ldots,v^{\dagger}_{J}\},\>Y^{\dagger}:=\{y^{\dagger}_{1},\ldots,y^{\dagger}_{J}\}
.
We recommend that the reader review these conventions.

10.1
State Estimation

In Subsections
10.1.1
and
10.1.2
, respectively, we show how the 3DVar and 4DVar
algorithms may be amortized. We employ amortized versions of the objective from Subsection
9.1.1
, using distance-like deterministic scoring rules
\mathsf{d}
; see Definition
11.81
from Subsection
11.3.8
. In Subsection
10.1.3
we study amortization for estimators of the filter or smoother mean. We employ the expected squared error, which is a specific example of a
distance-like deterministic scoring rule, to define the objective function for amortization.

10.1.1
Amortized 3DVar

We consider 3DVar
1
1

1

More properly
cycled 3DVar
; see Remark
6.8
.
with the analysis step written in the optimization form (
6.25
), leading to the following algorithm:

(10.1a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j}),

(10.1b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(v),

(10.1c)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|v-\widehat{v}_{j+1}|^{2}_{\widehat{C}}+\frac{1}{2}|y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-h(v)|^{2}_{\Gamma}.

Our goal in this subsection is to make choices within the algorithm class
(
10.1
), or approximations of this algorithm class,
to improve recovery of the state
V^{\dagger}
from the observations
Y^{\dagger}
(using the notation from (
6.3
)).

Recall from Subsection
6.3.3
that, for linear
h(\cdot)=H\cdot
,
this algorithm reduces to

(10.2a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j}),

(10.2b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{j+1}+K\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-H\widehat{v}_{j+1}}}\right),

where
K=\widehat{C}H^{\top}(H\widehat{C}H^{\top}+\Gamma)^{-1}
. This suggests learning

\widehat{C}
, or
K
, to optimize the algorithm; indeed, the idea of learning
K
is pursued in Subsection
9.2.1
. In this subsection,
motivated by the setting where
h
is nonlinear,
we describe learning strategies that work within generalizations
of the class of algorithms (
10.2
).

Amortized 3DVar: Nonlinear Dependence On Innovation

Here we postulate an explicit form of the map
\widehat{v}_{j+1}\mapsto v_{j+1},
side-stepping the solution of the
optimization problem (
10.1
). To this end, we introduce a learnable
nonlinear

gain function,
which takes the innovation as input:

(10.3a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j}),

(10.3b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{j+1}+\mathsf{k}\Bigl(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-h\bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}\bigr);\theta\Bigr).

Thus, we drop the optimization formulation of 3DVar in (
10.1
), and instead seek to learn
\mathsf{k}(\cdot\,;\theta):\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
in (
10.3
). Function
\mathsf{k}(\cdot\,;\theta)
is a (possibly nonlinear) map for each fixed
\theta\in\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
that applies the state correction at each assimilation time based on the observed data and, in particular, the innovation.
This is a specific subclass of the algorithm class defined in (
9.2
). Moreover, it reduces to the classical ansatz made for 3DVar in (
6.23
) when the map
\mathsf{k}
is a linear function of the innovation.
We aim to learn
\theta
given the following assumption about available data.

Data Assumption 10.1
.

We are given
\{\big((V^{\dagger})^{(n)},(Y^{\dagger})^{(n)}\big)\}_{n=1}^{N}
,
comprising multiple independent realizations from the joint
distribution on
(V^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger})
defined by (
9.1
).

Recall the objective function in (
9.11
) that measures the distance between the estimated state
v_{j}(\theta)
and true state
v^{\dagger}_{j}
, both depending on specific realizations of data. Here we define

\bigl\{\{v_{j}^{(n)}(\theta)\}_{j=0}^{J}\bigr\}_{n=1}^{N}

to be the set of state sequences from the algorithm (
10.3
), when driven by the set of observation sequences
\{(y^{\dagger}_{j})^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
, for each
j\in\{1,\dots,J\}
; this set of state sequence estimators
depends on the gain parameter
\theta
, as made explicit in the notation. The initial conditions
\{v_{0}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
may be chosen, for instance, by sampling the Gaussian initial distribution
\mathcal{N}(m_{0},C_{0}).

We average the objective function in (
9.11
) over the multiple realizations of sequences indexed by
n
; minimization defines the optimal
\theta^{\star}.
Thus, we obtain the optimization problem

(10.4a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{J,N}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{JN}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathsf{d}\bigl(v_{j}^{(n)}(\theta),(v^{\dagger}_{j})^{(n)}\bigr),

(10.4b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}^{J,N}(\theta).

Remark 10.2
.

Here we are solving the supervised learning problem of recovering the state
V^{\dagger}
from the
observations
Y^{\dagger}.
We have imposed a sequential filtering structure, via the architecture
(
10.3
), on the learned solution. It is natural to ask whether a smoothing-type
architecture could also be learned, in the context of 4DVar.
The next subsection considers such approaches but first, in this subsection, we consider another
approach to amortizing 3DVar.
∎

Amortized 3DVar: Learning Optimization Step

We now return to the optimization formulation (
10.1
) of 3DVar.
We consider fixed
\widehat{C}
; see the original derivation as described in
Subsection
9.2.1
. We then explicitly learn the solution of the optimization
problem embedded in algorithm (
10.1
). To this end, we isolate the optimization step and define

(10.5a)

\displaystyle v_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathsf{J}}(v),

(10.5b)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|v-w|^{2}_{\widehat{C}}+\frac{1}{2}|y^{\dagger}-h(v)|^{2}_{\Gamma}.

Noting that the solution of this optimization problem, for fixed
\widehat{C}
, depends
on
(w,y^{\dagger})
, we write
v_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}=v_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}(w,y^{\dagger}).
We now discuss learning the function
v_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}.

Remark 10.3
.

The subscript
{\mathrm{MAP}}
denotes the fact that the optimization problem (
10.5
) defines a maximum
a posteriori
estimator for a Bayesian inverse problem with prior
\rho(v)=\mathcal{N}(w,\widehat{C})
and likelihood
\mathsf{l}(y^{\dagger}|v)=\mathcal{N}(h(v),\Gamma)
; see Remark
6.9
.
∎

Data Assumption 10.4
.

We are given
\{(w^{(n)},(y^{\dagger})^{(n)},v_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}^{(n)})\}_{n=1}^{N}
drawn i.i.d. from a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}.

We use this data to learn an approximation to the function
v_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}(w,y^{\dagger}).

With this goal in mind, we introduce parameter set
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}

and let
\mathcal{R}_{\Theta}\subset C(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k},\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote
a parameterized set of functions
v_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}^{\mathsf{a}}(\cdot\,,\cdot\,;\theta)
for
\theta\in\Theta.

The comments in Remark
5.5
generalize to the situation here,
as does Example
5.6
. The main issue is the choice of the probability measure from which the training data in Data Assumption
10.4
are drawn.
Once the training data for
(w,y^{\dagger})

are well chosen, the corresponding
v_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}
data may be found by solving the optimization problem (
10.5
). Then, supervised learning may be employed to determine
\theta^{\star}
leading to the approximation

v_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}^{\mathsf{a}}(\cdot\,,\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})\approx v_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}(\cdot\,,\cdot)
from within
\mathcal{R}_{\Theta}.

This leads to the following variant on the (cycled) 3DVar algorithm:

(10.6a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j}),

(10.6b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}

\displaystyle=v_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}^{\mathsf{a}}\Bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1},y^{\dagger}_{j+1};\theta^{\star}\Bigr).

10.1.2
Amortized 4DVar

Recall the 4DVar approach to the smoothing problem defined in Section
6.4.2
.
The methodology defines an estimate of the state
V_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}:=\{(v_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}})_{0},\ldots,(v_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}})_{J}\}

from the observed data
Y^{\dagger}:=\{y^{\dagger}_{1},\ldots,y^{\dagger}_{J}\},
through the solution of an
optimization problem. Emphasizing the dependence of the objective function
{\mathsf{J}}
and the solution
V_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}
on the available data
Y^{\dagger}
we have

(10.7a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(V;Y^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{R}(V)+\mathsf{L}(V;Y^{\dagger}),

(10.7b)

\displaystyle V_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}(Y^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{d(J+1)}}{\mathsf{J}}(V;Y^{\dagger}),

where
\mathsf{R}(V)
and
\mathsf{L}(V;Y^{\dagger})
are defined in (
6.67
) and (
6.68
), respectively.
The following two subsections parallel Subsections

5.1.1
and
5.1.2
, where we discuss
two different approaches to learning the MAP estimator
defined by a Bayesian inverse problem.

Amortized 4DVar Using Only Observations

Here we discuss an approach to amortize 4DVar based on the methodology of Subsection
5.1.1
for amortizing MAP estimation. To do so we make the following data assumption
2
2

2

Again recall the notation from Remark
7.1
.
:

Data Assumption 10.5
.

We are given
\{(Y^{\dagger})^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
, which are multiple independent realizations from the marginal distribution
\kappa

on the observed data
Y^{\dagger}:=\{y^{\dagger}_{1},\ldots,y^{\dagger}_{J}\}

defined by (
9.1
).

Using the 4DVar objective function (
10.7a
), we learn an amortized 4DVar estimator
V^{\star}(\cdot):=V(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})
by finding the
\theta^{\star}
that minimizes this objective in expectation over the observation realizations:

(10.8a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}_{\mathsf{obs}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{Y^{\dagger}\sim\kappa}\Bigl[{\mathsf{J}}(V(Y^{\dagger};\theta);Y^{\dagger})\Bigr],

(10.8b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}_{\mathsf{obs}}(\theta).

In practice, we use the empirical measure defined by
\{(Y^{\dagger})^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}

to approximate the expectation defining
{\mathsf{J}}_{\mathsf{obs}}(\cdot)
and minimize the resulting
approximate objective function.

Amortized 4DVar Using Supervised Learning

Here we assume that we have multiple solutions of the 4DVar MAP estimator
for multiple realizations of the data
Y^{\dagger}.
Our goal is to learn the dependence of

V_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}(\cdot)
on
Y^{\dagger}.

Data Assumption 10.6
.

We are given
\{(Y^{\dagger})^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
,
comprising multiple independent realizations from the marginal distribution
\kappa
on the observed data
Y^{\dagger}:=\{y^{\dagger}_{1},\ldots,y^{\dagger}_{J}\}
defined by (
6.1
), (
6.2
), together with solutions
(V_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}})^{(n)}=V_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}\bigl((Y^{\dagger})^{(n)}\bigr)
defined by (
10.7
).

If the optimization problem (
10.7
) has a unique solution then the
\{\big(V_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}})^{(n)}\big)\}_{n=1}^{N}
are distributed according to the pushforward of
\kappa
under the map from observation
Y^{\dagger}
to minimizer
V_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}
.
Given the data
\bigl\{\big((Y^{\dagger})^{(n)},(V_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}})^{(n)}\big)\bigr\}_{n=1}^{N}
from Data Assumption
10.6

we may apply the methodology of Subsection
5.1.2
to
learn the mapping from
Y^{\dagger}
to
V_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}
. Let
\theta^{\star}
solve
(in practice an empirical approximation of) the minimization problem

(10.9a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{Y^{\dagger}\sim\kappa}\Bigl[\mathsf{d}\bigl(V(Y^{\dagger};\theta),V_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MAP}}}(Y^{\dagger})\bigr)\Bigr],

(10.9b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).

Then, we identify the function
V^{\star}(\cdot):=V(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})
as our solution to the amortized MAP estimation problem.

10.1.3
Amortized Posterior Mean Estimation

In this subsection we consider learning an approximation of the map from observed data to the posterior mean of
either the filter or the smoother. We do this by minimizing the squared error between a state estimate and a trajectory, in expectation over the joint distribution of states and observations. We will show that this results in a cost function that is minimized at the mean of the filtering or smoothing distribution. This is an amortized version of the methodology introduced in Subsection
9.1.1
, with a particular choice of deterministic scoring rule. It is also an application of the methodology described in Subsection
5.2.4
for amortized posterior mean estimation in Bayesian inverse problems, applied to filtering and smoothing.

Learning the Filter Mean

We make the following assumption about the available data.

Data Assumption 10.7
.

For some given
j\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
we have access to data set
\bigl\{\bigl((V^{\dagger}_{j})^{(n)},(Y^{\dagger}_{j})^{(n)}\bigl)\bigr\}_{n\in\{1,\ldots,N\}}

generated as a realization of (
9.1
).

The following theorem provides the basis for learning state estimators using the squared error, using Data Assumption
10.7
.

Theorem 10.8
.

Let
m_{j}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j}]
be the mean of the filtering distribution, and let
v(Y^{\dagger}_{j})
denote any state estimator taking
Y^{\dagger}_{j}
as input. Then, with
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j})}

denoting expectation under the dynamics/observation model (
6.1
), (
6.2
), we have

(10.10)

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j})}\Bigl[|v(Y^{\dagger}_{j})-v^{\dagger}_{j}|^{2}\Bigr]\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j})}\Bigl[|m_{j}-v^{\dagger}_{j}|^{2}\Bigr].

Furthermore, the inequality is strict if

\mathbb{P}\bigl(v(Y^{\dagger}_{j})\neq m_{j}\bigr)>0,

where probability is taken with respect to the marginal distribution of

Y^{\dagger}_{j}
.

Proof.

This is a direct application of Theorem
5.9
.
∎

Remark 10.9
.

As mentioned in Remark
1.18
, the posterior mean
is the Bayes estimator under squared-error objective;
that is, it minimizes the posterior expected squared error. Other objective functions
lead to other Bayes estimators. For example, an absolute-error
objective yields the posterior median for scalar-valued states, whilst a
component-wise absolute-error objective yields the coordinate-wise posterior
medians for vector-valued states. Theorem
10.8
extends
directly to these settings.
∎

Thus, having only the information
Y^{\dagger}_{j}
, the filter mean minimizes the expected squared error. Hence the squared error may be used as the basis for learning algorithms designed to match the true filter mean. We now minimize over a parameterized class of state estimators
v(Y^{\dagger}_{j};\theta)
, for
\theta\in\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
,

(10.11a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j})}\Bigl[|v(Y^{\dagger}_{j};\theta)-v^{\dagger}_{j}|^{2}\Bigr],

(10.11b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).

Remark 10.10
.

To implement the minimization of
{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)
, we use an empirical approximation. In particular, using Data Assumption
10.7

we may approximate the expectation
\mathbb{E}^{(v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j})}
using the pairs
\{\big((V^{\dagger}_{j})^{(n)},(Y^{\dagger}_{j})^{(n)}\big)\}_{n=1}^{N}
generated from the dynamical system (
9.1
). Assuming ergodicity (see Section
14.1
), moreover, implies that the expectation can instead be approximated using time averages over a long trajectory rather than multiple realizations of the data.
∎

Note that, in general, the parameterized models we learn will not recover the exact filter mean unless the class used is sufficiently rich to include it. Furthermore empiricalization, as discussed in the preceding remark,
leads to further discrepancy from the true posterior mean.

Learning the Smoother Mean

Theorem
10.8
is readily modified to apply to
the smoothing case. This also gives rise to a Bayes estimator.

Corollary 10.11
.

Let
V(Y^{\dagger})
be any state estimator taking
Y^{\dagger}
as input. Then

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(V^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger})}\Bigl[|V(Y^{\dagger})-V^{\dagger}|^{2}\Bigr]\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(V^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger})}\Bigl[|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[V^{\dagger}|Y^{\dagger}]-V^{\dagger}|^{2}\Bigr].

Furthermore, the inequality is strict if

\mathbb{P}\Bigl(V(Y^{\dagger})\neq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[V^{\dagger}|Y^{\dagger}]\Bigr)>0,

where probability is taken with respect to the marginal distribution of

Y^{\dagger}
.

We now again make Data Assumption
10.1
.
Using Corollary
10.11
suggests learning
state estimators
V(Y^{\dagger};\theta)
to estimate the sequence of states

V^{\dagger}
, for
\theta\in\Theta
, using the minimization problem

(10.12a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(V^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger})}\Bigl[|V(Y^{\dagger};\theta)-V^{\dagger}|^{2}\Bigr],

(10.12b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).

As for learning the filter mean, an empirical minimization of
{\mathsf{J}}
may be implemented in practice using the data from Data Assumption
10.1
, or through time averages over long trajectories under the assumption of ergodicity.

10.2
Variational Inference Approaches

In this section we build on the variational inference approach to data assimilation described
in Chapter
7
. We study amortized versions of variational approaches
to both filtering, in Subsection
10.2.1
, and smoothing
in Subsection
10.2.2
.

10.2.1
Amortized Variational Filtering

Here we build on the material in Subsection
7.2
to include the amortization afforded
by working under Data Assumption
10.5
. To that end we let
\mathbb{k}_{j+1}
denote the marginal of
\kappa
on

Y^{\dagger}_{j+1}.
As in Subsection
7.2
we let
{\mathsf{A}}_{\theta}(\cdot\,;y^{\dagger})
denote a
\theta
-parameterized
family of approximate analysis maps, recalling prediction and analysis maps
{\mathsf{P}}
and
{\mathsf{A}}(\cdot\,;y^{\dagger})
defined
in Subsection
6.2.1
. Using prediction and approximate analysis steps we may define a sequence,
in
j
, of approximate filtering distributions
q_{j}(\theta)
. Arguing identically to Lemma
7.6

shows that the algorithms we study have the property that
q_{j}(\theta)
depends only on
Y^{\dagger}_{j},
the desired filtering
structure. Throughout this subsection, we leave this dependence implicit in the notation. Again, as in Subsection
7.2
, our goal is to choose
\theta\in\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
so that
q_{j}(\theta)
approximates the filtering distribution
\pi_{j}
.

Generalizing (
7.8
) to include amortization over multiple data instances, we obtain

(10.13a)

\displaystyle q^{\star}_{0}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0},

(10.13b)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{\prime}_{j+1}(\theta;Y^{\dagger}_{j+1})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(q_{j+1}(\theta;Y^{\dagger}_{j+1})\|{\mathsf{P}}q^{\star}_{j}(Y^{\dagger}_{j})\bigr)-\mathbb{E}^{{q_{j+1}(\theta;Y^{\dagger}_{j+1})}}[\log\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|\cdot)]

(10.13c)

\displaystyle q_{j+1}(\theta;Y^{\dagger}_{j+1})

\displaystyle={\mathsf{A}}_{\theta}({\mathsf{P}}q^{\star}_{j}(Y^{\dagger}_{j});y^{\dagger}_{j+1}),

(10.13d)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{Y^{\dagger}_{j+1}\sim\mathbb{k}_{j+1}}\bigl[{\mathsf{J}}^{\prime}_{j+1}(\theta;Y^{\dagger}_{j+1})\bigr],

(10.13e)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}_{j+1}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(\theta),

(10.13f)

\displaystyle q^{\star}_{j+1}(Y^{\dagger}_{j+1})

\displaystyle={\mathsf{A}}_{\theta^{\star}_{j+1}}({\mathsf{P}}q^{\star}_{j}(Y^{\dagger}_{j});y^{\dagger}_{j+1}).

In practice, the expectation over
\mathbb{k}_{j+1}
will be approximated empirically, using Data Assumption
10.5
.

Again, as in Subsection
7.2
, we may invoke an ergodic hypothesis and define

(10.14)

\theta^{\star}=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\theta^{\star}_{j}

and consider the approximate filter

(10.15)

q_{j+1}={\mathsf{A}}_{\theta^{\star}}({\mathsf{P}}q_{j};y^{\dagger}_{j+1}),\quad q_{0}=\pi_{0}.

for
q_{j}\approx q_{j}^{\prime}.
This approximate filter may then be used on new data sequences

Y^{\dagger}
, different from the specific sequences appearing in Data Assumption
10.5

and used to determine
\theta^{\star}.
Because of the amortization across both time
j
and
data realizations
n
this is likely to be an improvement, in terms of generalization
to new observation sequences, over the methodology in Subsection
7.2
.

A variant on the methodology following from (
10.13
) is to use the
following averaged minimization problem

(10.16a)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(\theta),

(10.16b)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{Y^{\dagger}_{j+1}\sim\mathbb{k}_{j+1}}\bigl[\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(q_{j+1}(\theta)\|{\mathsf{P}}q_{j}(\theta)\bigr)-\mathbb{E}^{{q_{j+1}(\theta)}}[\log\mathbb{P}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|\cdot)]\bigr],

(10.16c)

\displaystyle q_{j+1}(\theta)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{A}}_{\theta}({\mathsf{P}}q_{j}(\theta);y^{\dagger}_{j+1}),\quad q_{0}=\pi_{0}.

Example 10.12
.

Consider making the choice that
q_{j}
is Gaussian
\mathcal{N}(m_{j},C_{j})
. To implement either of the methodologies outlined above
we can proceed as in Section
7.5
, using
{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{G}}
, as defined by (
7.16
a).
This forces the predicted filtering distribution to remain
on the manifold of Gaussians, after each prediction step. We then replace
{\mathsf{P}}
in (
10.13
) or (
10.16
c) by
{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{G}}
.

We can then define
{\mathsf{A}}_{\theta}
appearing in (
10.16
c) by the Gaussian update
rules (
7.16
b), (
7.16
c); parameter
\theta
is the
gain matrix
K
. In the case of
{\mathsf{A}}_{\theta_{j+1}}
appearing in (
10.13
d)
we may again use the Gaussian updates (
7.16
b), (
7.16
c), but
with
K\mapsto\theta_{j+1}:=K_{j+1}.

∎

10.2.2
Amortized Variational Smoothing

We may also be interested in learning a parametric model to capture dependence of an approximate smoothing distribution on the data
Y^{\dagger}
, an amortized version of the ideas in Section
7.1
. To this end, in this subsection we work under Data Assumption
10.5
. We generalize the previous subsection and seek to find smoothing
distributions, dependent on data
Y^{\dagger}
, from the set
\mathcal{Q}^{J}\subset\mathcal{P}^{J}
. To define
\mathcal{Q}^{J}
we consider minimizing over the
\theta
-parameterized family of maps

\mathcal{S}_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{Jk}\to\mathcal{Q}^{J},\qquad\mathcal{S}_{\theta}(Y^{\dagger})=q(\cdot\,;Y^{\dagger},\theta),

where
\theta\in\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
:

(10.17a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(q)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{Y^{\dagger}\sim\kappa}\Bigl[\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q(\cdot\,;Y^{\dagger})\|\rho)-\mathbb{E}^{q(\cdot\,;Y^{\dagger})}\bigl[\log\mathsf{l}(Y^{\dagger}|\cdot)\bigr]\Bigr],

(10.17b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}\bigl(\mathcal{S}_{\theta}\bigr).

Then, for new, previously unseen data
(Y^{\dagger})^{\prime}
, we choose our approximate smoothing distribution to be
q^{\star}=\mathcal{S}_{\theta^{\star}}((Y^{\dagger})^{\prime}).
We emphasize that the resulting smoothing distribution
does not enforce the temporal dependence in filtering where each state marginal depends on past observations. In particular,
in the notation of Section
7.3
, we typically expect that
q^{\star}\in\mathcal{P}^{J}\backslash\mathcal{C}^{J}.

We consider an example of amortized variational smoothing which is similar
in approach to that outlined for Bayesian inversion in Section
2.1
; see Example
2.12
for Gaussian variational inference with a specific likelihood.
However, we propose a different two-stage approach to the optimization problem;
this two-stage approach could have been adopted in Example
2.12
and likewise
the one-stage approach of Example
2.12
could be adopted here.

Example 10.13
.

In Section
7.4
we discuss the use of
variational smoothing to find a Gaussian approximation centered on the
MAP estimator. We now generalize this approach to the amortized setting.
We use a two-stage procedure. First, using the ideas of
Subsection
10.1.2
, we determine an amortized MAP estimator

V(Y^{\dagger};\theta^{\star})
that captures its dependence on the observations

Y^{\dagger}
. We then use amortized variational smoothing to determine an
observation-dependent covariance for the Gaussian approximation.

To this end, let
\Theta_{\mathsf{A}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}
and introduce
functions

\mathsf{A}(\cdot\,;\vartheta):\mathbb{R}^{Jk}\to\mathbb{R}^{(J+1)d\times(J+1)d},\qquad\vartheta\in\Theta_{\mathsf{A}}.

For example,
\mathsf{A}(Y^{\dagger};\vartheta)
may be restricted to be lower
triangular. We require that

\det\bigl(\mathsf{A}(Y^{\dagger};\vartheta)\bigr)>0

for every
Y^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{Jk}
and
\vartheta\in\Theta_{\mathsf{A}}
. Define
the map
\mathcal{S}_{\vartheta}:\mathbb{R}^{Jk}\to\mathcal{P}^{J}
by

\mathcal{S}_{\vartheta}(Y^{\dagger})=\mathcal{N}\Bigl(V(Y^{\dagger};\theta^{\star}),\mathsf{A}(Y^{\dagger};\vartheta)\mathsf{A}(Y^{\dagger};\vartheta)^{\top}\Bigr).

We identify the variational approximation by solving

(10.18)

\vartheta^{\star}\in\argmin_{\vartheta\in\Theta_{\mathsf{A}}}{\mathsf{J}}\bigl(\mathcal{S}_{\vartheta}\bigr),

where
{\mathsf{J}}
is defined in (
10.17
). Then, for new,
previously unseen data
(Y^{\dagger})^{\prime}
, we define the approximate smoothing
distribution by

q^{\star}=\mathcal{S}_{\vartheta^{\star}}\bigl((Y^{\dagger})^{\prime}\bigr).

∎

10.3
Learning via Scoring Rule Minimization

In this section we use objectives based on expected strictly proper
scores to learn the filtering distributions.
The methodology may be viewed as an application to filtering of the methodology for amortizing posterior estimation in Bayesian inverse problems that is described in Subsection
5.2.4
. By invoking ergodicity to approximate the
expectation we will make a connection to the methodology introduced in Subsection
9.1.2
, which does not
involve amortization over multiple realizations from the dynamics-observation model (
6.1
), (
6.2
),
but just uses one long time-series. In Subsection
10.3.1
we introduce the general formulation,
specifying to transport maps in Subsection
10.3.2
.

10.3.1
General Formulation

The following theorem provides the basis for learning filters using strictly proper scoring rules from Definition
11.49
.

Theorem 10.14
.

Consider a strictly proper scoring rule
\mathsf{S}(\cdot,\cdot):\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}.
Let
\pi_{j}(v^{\dagger}_{j})=\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})
be the filtering distribution
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
q(\cdot\,;Y^{\dagger}_{j})
denote any distribution
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
parameterized by
Y^{\dagger}_{j}.
Then, with
\mathbb{E}^{(v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j})}

denoting expectation under the dynamics/observation model (
6.1
), (
6.2
), we have

(10.19)

\mathbb{E}^{(v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j})}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}(q(\cdot\,;Y^{\dagger}_{j}),v^{\dagger}_{j})\Bigr]\geq\mathbb{E}^{(v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j})}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}(\pi_{j},v^{\dagger}_{j})\Bigr].

Furthermore, the inequality is strict if

\mathbb{P}\bigl(q(\cdot\,;Y^{\dagger}_{j})\neq\pi_{j}\bigr)>0,

where probability is taken with respect to the marginal distribution of

Y^{\dagger}_{j}
.

Proof.

This is a direct application of Theorem
5.20
.
∎

Thus, having only the information
Y^{\dagger}_{j}
, the filtering distribution minimizes the expected score. Hence this expected score may be used as the basis for learning algorithms designed to match
the true filter, using only samples from the joint distribution of
v^{\dagger}_{j}
and
Y^{\dagger}_{j}
.
Certain scoring rules are amenable to sample-based implementation, which will be convenient in what follows.

We now define a learning problem for the filtering distributions. Motivated by (
7.10
), we parameterize a set of approximate filters
q
of the form:

(10.20)

q_{j+1}(\theta)={\mathsf{A}}_{\theta}({\mathsf{P}}q_{j}(\theta);y^{\dagger}_{j+1}),\quad q_{0}=\pi_{0},

where
{\mathsf{A}}_{\theta}
denotes an approximation of the true analysis map
{\mathsf{A}}
, parameterized by
\theta\in\Theta
;
map
{\mathsf{A}}_{\theta}
depends on the approximate forecast distribution and the observation
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
and computes the filtering distribution. The following result shows that if set of parameters
\Theta
is large enough to represent the true analysis map given in (
6.8
), then
the learning problem that minimizes the scoring rule will recover the true filtering distribution.

Corollary 10.15
.

Consider the distribution
q_{j}
evolving under the
\theta
-parameterized algorithm
(
7.6
) and define the optimization problem

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{(v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j})}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}({\mathsf{A}}_{\theta}({\mathsf{P}}\pi_{j-1}(\theta);y^{\dagger}_{j}),v^{\dagger}_{j})\Bigr],

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).

Then, if there is some
\theta\in\Theta
for which
{\mathsf{A}}_{\theta}={\mathsf{A}}
and
j>0
, we have that

{\mathsf{J}}(\theta^{\star})=\mathbb{E}^{(v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j})}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}(\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j}),v^{\dagger}_{j})\Bigr].

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
10.14
by using (
10.20
) to replace
q(\cdot\,;Y^{\dagger}_{j})

by
q_{j}(\cdot\,;Y^{\dagger}_{j}),
computed from the true filtering distribition
\pi_{j-1}
at time
j-1.

∎

In principle we could implement an empirical approximation of the objective function implied
by the preceding corollary, under Data Assumption
10.7
. In practice this is complicated
by the dependence of
q_{j}(\theta)
on all preceding filtering steps. Implementation of the methodology requires empirical minimization. To this end we introduce:

Data Assumption 10.16
.

For some
J\in\mathbb{N}
we have access to one trajectory pair
\{(V^{\dagger}_{J},Y^{\dagger}_{J})\}
,
generated as a realization of (
9.1
).

Under this data assumption, invoking an ergodic hypothesis as discussed in Remark
10.10

leads to the empirical optimization problem

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{J}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}({\mathsf{A}}_{\theta}({\mathsf{P}}q_{j-1}(\theta);y^{\dagger}_{j}),v^{\dagger}_{j})\Bigr],

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}^{J}(\theta).

This is precisely the form we have already introduced in Subsection
9.1.2
. In the bibliographic remarks in
Section
10.5
we refer to theory justifying this form of objective function.

10.3.2
Transport-Based Formulation

We now consider using strictly proper scoring rules to learn a parameterized transport map
T\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\Theta\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
that approximately maps a sample from the forecast distribution and an observation to a sample from the filtering distribution. This is a special case of the transport-based methodology for learning amortized prior-to-posterior maps using strictly proper scoring rules from Subsection
5.2.4
.

This can be achieved by writing the action of the parameterized forecast-to-analysis map by pushing forward the forecast distribution under an observation-dependent transport map:

(10.21)

{\mathsf{A}}_{\theta}(\widehat{\pi},y)=T(\cdot,y;\theta)_{\sharp}\widehat{\pi}.

This results in
the following sequential ensemble algorithm, which alternates the forecast step with an analysis step (similarly to (
9.30
)) that is implemented using transport:

(10.22a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j}^{(n)})+\xi^{(n)}_{j},\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(10.22b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=T\Bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},y^{\dagger}_{j+1};\theta\Bigr),\quad n=1,\ldots,N.

If we assume that the
\{v_{j}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
are drawn
from an approximate filter
\pi_{j}^{N}
at time
j
,
then writing the analysis ensemble at time
j+1
as

\pi^{N}_{j+1}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{v_{j+1}^{(n)}},

the optimization problem in Corollary
10.15
becomes

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(v^{\dagger}_{j+1},Y^{\dagger}_{j+1})}[\mathsf{S}(\pi_{j+1}^{N},v^{\dagger}_{j+1})],

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).

Implementing this optimization problem requires a scoring rule that can be evaluated for empirical measures; kernel scores (Definition
11.74
), which include the energy score (Definition
11.51
), are examples of such scoring rules. In practice, the expectation over
(v^{\dagger}_{j+1},Y^{\dagger}_{j+1})
can be approximated using ergodicity as discussed at the end of the preceding subsection.

10.4
Online Transport Approaches to Filtering and Smoothing

In this section we seek transports that map to each filtering distribution
\pi_{j+1}
, as in Subsections
9.3.2
and
9.3.3
. In this case, however, we seek a transport for each assimilation step that is computed
online
, meaning that it only uses data available at the given assimilation step. This is in contrast to earlier Sections
10.1
–
10.3
in this chapter, which approximate the filtering distribution
offline
– using variational inference in Section
10.2
and strictly proper scoring rules in Section
10.3
–
by amortizing over multiple sequences of observations. These latter approaches update the filtering distribution on the basis of online observations. They assume that observations given online to evaluate the approximation are drawn from the same distribution as the data given offline for learning. On the other hand, the approximations we construct in this subsection only use online data and do not make this assumption. This may be particularly desirable in generalizations of (
9.1
) where the observation likelihood or model dynamics are time-dependent.

We start, in Subsection
10.4.1
, with some general considerations.
In Subsection
10.4.2
we minimize general strictly proper scoring rules, with the energy score as an example,
as in Subsection
9.3.2
where dependence on the observed data is not learned; in Subsection
10.4.3
we leverage the transport methodology developed in Chapter
4
, based on maximum likelihood.
The ideas in Subsection
10.4.2
are also related to Subsection
5.2.4
, where amortized transports are learned to sample the posterior distributions of Bayesian inference problems, using strictly proper scoring rules.
Lastly, we extend this framework of learning transports to smoothing in Subsection
10.4.4
.

10.4.1
General Considerations

Here, we will focus on approaches to perform inference at each assimilation step using a
j
-dependent map

T_{j+1}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

that
explicitly
depends on both the predicted state
\widehat{v}_{j+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the true observation
y_{j+1}^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. A separate map is estimated at each time step, and thus we emphasize this dependence on
j
with the subscript.
This results in the following sequential algorithm, which alternates the forecast step with an analysis step (similarly to (
9.30
)) that is implemented using transport:

(10.23a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j}^{(n)})+\xi^{(n)}_{j},\quad n=1,\ldots,N,

(10.23b)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=T_{j+1}\Bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},y^{\dagger}_{j+1};\theta_{j+1}\Bigr),\quad n=1,\ldots,N.

where
T_{j+1}
depends on parameters
\theta_{j+1}\in\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
.
Our goal is to find the parameters of the map
T_{j+1}
, so that

(10.24)

\widehat{v}_{j+1}\sim\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}\Rightarrow T_{j+1}(\widehat{v}_{j+1},y^{\dagger}_{j+1};\theta_{j+1})\sim\pi_{j+1},

where we have emphasized the dependence of the map and its (separate) parameters
\theta_{j+1}
on the index
j+1
.

To achieve this goal without samples from the filtering distribution, we will learn a map
T_{j+1}(\cdot,\cdot;\theta)\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}

that amortizes over the observations and can sample from the conditional distribution
\mathbb{P}(v_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j},y_{j+1})

corresponding to the prior defined by the forecast distribution
\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}(v_{j+1})
and the likelihood defined by
\mathsf{l}(y_{j+1}|\widehat{v}_{j+1})
for
any
observation
y_{j+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. That is,
we wish to approximately satisfy

\widehat{v}_{j+1}\sim\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}\Rightarrow T_{j+1}\Bigl(\widehat{v}_{j+1},y_{j+1};\theta_{j+1}\Bigr)\sim\mathbb{P}(v_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j},y_{j+1}),

for any
y_{j+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}.

We note that this is a different purpose for amortization than earlier sections in this chapter, which use amortization to learn a map that can generalize to new true observations
y_{j+1}^{\dagger}
across time steps
j
. Here, we learn the map’s dependence under the following data assumption which assumes only access to an ensemble from the joint distribution of state
\widehat{v}_{j+1}
and synthetic observations
\widehat{y}_{j+1}
at step
j+1
. The synthetic observations are generated from the state using the observation model
\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)}=h(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)})+\eta^{(n)}_{j+1}
for
n=1,\ldots,N
. In this section, the ability to make likelihood evaluations is not assumed, thereby making the approach
likelihood-free
.

Data Assumption 10.17
.

We are given pairs of i.i.d. samples
\{(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},{\widehat{y}}_{j+1}^{(n)})\}_{n=1}^{N}
from the joint distribution
\gamma_{j+1}(v_{j+1},y_{j+1})\coloneqq\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}(v_{j+1})\mathsf{l}(y_{j+1}|v_{j+1})
.

Remark 10.18
.

In practice we will estimate the map’s parameters, meaning that analysis step (
10.24
) will only generate approximate posterior samples. As a result, the forecast samples at the next step will not be exact forecast samples from
\gamma_{j+1}(v_{j+1},y_{j+1})
because
\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}(v_{j+1})
will have been approximated.
This will typically produce an error accumulation across time. We do not study this error
propagation here. For simplicity we will assume, in this section,
that we can sample
\gamma_{j+1}(v_{j+1},y_{j+1})
exactly, as in Data Assumption
10.17
.
∎

In the following two subsections we will present two formulations to approximate map
T_{j+1}
and hence
to approximately enforce (
10.24
).

10.4.2
Online Minimization of Scoring Rules

The filtering distribution
\pi_{j+1}
is found from
Bayes Theorem
1.3
with the prior given by
the forecast
\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}
and the likelihood model given by
\mathsf{l}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}|\cdot)
.
Using the pushforward notation, our goal is to choose
\theta_{j+1}
to achieve the pushforward condition

(10.25)

T_{j+1}(\cdot,y^{\dagger}_{j+1};\theta_{j+1})_{\sharp}\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}=\pi_{j+1}.

We introduce the approach of this subsection with the following theorem.

Theorem 10.19
.

Consider a strictly proper scoring rule
\mathsf{S}(\cdot,\cdot):\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}.
Let
\pi_{j+1}(v_{j+1}|y_{j+1})=\mathbb{P}(v_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j},y_{j+1})
be the filtering distribution
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
given an observation
y_{j+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
at time
j+1
and let
q(\cdot\,;y_{j+1})
denote any distribution
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
parameterized by
y_{j+1}.
Then,

(10.26)

\mathbb{E}^{(v_{j+1},y_{j+1})\sim\gamma_{j+1}}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}(q(\cdot\,;y_{j+1}),v_{j+1})\Bigr]\geq\mathbb{E}^{(v_{j+1},y_{j+1})\sim\gamma_{j+1}}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}(\pi_{j+1}(\cdot|y_{j+1}),v_{j+1})\Bigr].

Furthermore, equality is achieved if
q(\cdot\,;y_{j+1})=\pi_{j+1}(\cdot|y_{j+1})
for
\kappa_{j+1}
-almost every
y_{j+1}
.

Proof.

Noting that
\gamma_{j+1}(v_{j+1},y_{j+1})=\pi_{j+1}(v_{j+1}|y_{j+1})\kappa_{j+1}(y_{j+1})
,
this follows similarly to the proof of Theorem
5.20
.
∎

By parameterizing the class of approximate distributions as

q(\cdot;y_{j+1},\theta)=T_{j+1}(\cdot,y_{j+1};\theta)_{\sharp}\widehat{\pi}_{j+1},

we obtain the following objective function for the transport map parameters, motivated by Theorem
10.19
:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{(v_{j+1},y_{j+1})\sim\gamma_{j+1}}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}(T_{j+1}(\cdot,y_{j+1};\theta)_{\sharp}\widehat{\pi}_{j+1},v_{j+1})\Bigr].

This allows us to use the joint ensemble for
\gamma_{j+1}
arising from Data Assumption
10.17
to define the following empirical optimization problem for the map parameters:

(10.27a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{N}_{j+1}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathsf{S}(\pi^{N}_{j+1}(\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)};\theta),\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}),

(10.27b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}_{j+1}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}^{N}_{j+1}(\theta),

where
\pi^{N}_{j+1}
represents an empirical measure for the pushforward samples, which depends on the map parameters and an observation
\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)}
. That is,

(10.28)

\pi^{N}_{j+1}(\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)};\theta)=\frac{1}{N-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m=1\\
m\neq n\end{subarray}}^{N}\delta_{T_{j+1}(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(m)},\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)};\theta)}.

Remark 10.20
.

The exclusion of
n=m
in (
10.28
) is needed because
\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(m)}
and
\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(m)}
are paired and therefore dependent, whereas the forecast sample entering the pushforward distribution should be independent of the conditioning pair. ∎

As in Subsection
10.3.2
a scoring rule that can be evaluated on empirical measures,
defined by Data Assumption
10.17
, must be used to carry out the minimization.

Example 10.21
.

We employ ideas similar to those underlying empirical approximation of the energy distance and MMD in
Remarks
11.29
and
11.30
.
Picking
\mathsf{S}
to be the energy score (Definition
11.51
), we obtain the objective for (
10.27a
) given by

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}_{j+1}^{N}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N(N-1)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n=1\\
m\neq n\end{subarray}}^{N}|T_{j+1}(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(m)};\theta)-\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(m)}|

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2N(N-1)(N-2)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l,m,n=1\\
l\neq m,l\neq n,m\neq n\end{subarray}}^{N}|T_{j+1}(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(l)};\theta)-T_{j+1}(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(m)},\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(l)};\theta)|.

The expectations in each term of the objective function involve a tensor product of distributions for the states and observations. These expectations are estimated from a single ensemble of paired states and observations by permuting the pairings. For instance,
(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)},\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(l)})\sim\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}\otimes\kappa_{j+1}
for
n\neq l
.
∎

10.4.3
Maximum Likelihood Estimation

In this subsection we consider an alternative approach to construct an observation-dependent transport. To do so, we first identify a transport map
that pushes forward
the joint distribution
\gamma_{j+1}(y,v)
to the filtering distribution
\pi_{j+1}(v)

by composing transport maps that can be estimated using the maximum likelihood approach introduced in Chapter
5
. We continue to work under Data Assumption
10.17
.
See Remark
13.4
for the connection between KL divergence minimization and maximum likelihood and Section
13.2
for more information on density estimation via transport.

Recall from Remark
7.1
that
\kappa_{j+1}(y)

denotes the marginal of joint distribution
\gamma_{j+1}(y,v)
.
Let
\varrho(v)
be a reference density on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, such as a standard Gaussian. The following theorem shows how to construct a transport that pushes forward the joint distribution
on the state and observation,
\gamma_{j+1}
,
to the filtering distribution
\pi_{j+1}
, based on a map that pushes forward any conditional distribution
\mathbb{P}(v|y)
of
\gamma_{j+1}
to the same reference distribution.

Theorem 10.22
.

Let

S_{j+1}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

be a transport map such that

S_{j+1}(y,\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

is invertible for every
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. We denote its inverse by

S_{j+1}(y,\cdot)^{-1}
.
Suppose that, for every
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, the map

S_{j+1}(y,\cdot)
pushes forward the conditional distribution

\mathbb{P}(v|y)
under
\gamma_{j+1}
to the reference distribution

\varrho
. Then, for every
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, the map

(10.29)

T_{j+1}(v,y,y^{\dagger}_{j+1})\coloneqq S_{j+1}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\cdot)^{-1}\bigl(S_{j+1}(y,v)\bigr)

pushes forward
\gamma_{j+1}
to the filtering distribution

\pi_{j+1}
corresponding to the observation
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
.

Proof.

Conditional on
y
, the random variable
S_{j+1}(y,v)
has
distribution
\varrho
given
v
drawn from the conditional distribution
\mathbb{P}(v|y)
under
\gamma_{j+1}
. Applying

S_{j+1}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\cdot)^{-1}
therefore produces a random variable
with distribution
\mathbb{P}(v|y^{\dagger}_{j+1})
, which is the desired
filtering distribution.
∎

Using the procedure in Subsection
5.2.3
,
and noting Remark
5.18
in particular, we identify the transport map
S(\cdot,\cdot\,;\theta)
, parameterized by
\theta\in\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
as the solution of the maximum likelihood problem

(10.30a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{(v_{j+1},y_{j+1})\sim\gamma_{j+1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log S_{j+1}(y_{j+1},\cdot\,;\theta)^{-1}_{\sharp}\varrho(v_{j+1})}}\right]

(10.30b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}_{j+1}

\displaystyle\in\argmax_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).

Given the joint ensemble of states and observations, as defined in Data Assumption
10.17
, we can define our desired transport map as the maximizer of the empirical objective:

(10.31)

\theta^{\star}_{j+1}\in\argmax_{\theta\in\Theta}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\log S_{j+1}(\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)},\cdot\,;\theta)^{-1}_{\sharp}\varrho(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}).

Using Theorem
10.22

we define the transport

T_{j+1}(v,y,y^{\dagger}_{j+1};\theta^{\star}_{j+1})\coloneqq S_{j+1}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\cdot\,;\theta^{\star}_{j+1})^{-1}(S_{j+1}(y,v;\theta^{\star}_{j+1})).

The transport is used for the sample update in (
9.3
c):

v_{j+1}^{(n)}=S_{j+1}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\cdot\,;\theta^{\star}_{j+1})^{-1}(S_{j+1}(\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)},\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)};\theta^{\star}_{j+1})),\qquad n=1,\dots,N.

We note that the dependence of each particle update on the entire ensemble is through
the parameter
\theta^{\star}_{j+1}.

Remark 10.23
.

The joint-to-posterior map depends on both the true observation
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
and synthetic observations
\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)}
at time
j+1
. For a fixed
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
, the transformation from the forecast to analysis state can be seen as a stochastic transport due to the randomness in the synthetic observations
\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)}
.
In contrast, for a fixed observation
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
, the transport in
Subsection
10.4.2
is a deterministic map that
pushes forward
\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}
to
\pi_{j+1}
.
∎

While the framework above is quite general, in practice we may want to seek transports within a parameterized family of functions with a particular structure. The following result shows that these filters based on composed maps are related to a mean-field version of the ensemble Kalman filter, discussed in the bibliography Section
10.5
.

Theorem 10.24
.

Assume that the reference distribution is

\varrho=\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
. Let

S(y,v;\theta)=A(v+By+c)

be an affine transport map that is invertible with respect to
v
for
every
y
, where
\theta=(A,B,c)
. Assume that the optimal parameter is
found by solving (
10.30
) over

\Theta=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{>},\;B\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k},\;c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\right\}.

Let
C_{\widehat{v}_{j+1},\widehat{y}_{j+1}}
and

C_{\widehat{y}_{j+1},\widehat{y}_{j+1}}
denote, respectively, the cross-covariance
of
(\widehat{v}_{j+1},\widehat{y}_{j+1})
and the covariance of
\widehat{y}_{j+1}
under

\gamma_{j+1}
. Assume that

C_{\widehat{y}_{j+1},\widehat{y}_{j+1}}
is invertible and that the residual
covariance

C_{\mathrm{res}}\coloneqq C_{\widehat{v}_{j+1},\widehat{v}_{j+1}}-C_{\widehat{v}_{j+1},\widehat{y}_{j+1}}C_{\widehat{y}_{j+1},\widehat{y}_{j+1}}^{-1}C_{\widehat{y}_{j+1},\widehat{v}_{j+1}}

is positive definite. Then the composed map
in (
10.29
) is the population analogue of the
ensemble Kalman filter
update (
6.37
b):
