T(v,y,y^{\dagger}_{j+1})=v+C_{\widehat{v}_{j+1},\widehat{y}_{j+1}}C_{\widehat{y}_{j+1},\widehat{y}_{j+1}}^{-1}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-y).

Proof.

For conciseness, we suppress the time subscripts in what follows.
By Theorem
5.19
, an optimal affine
transport is

S(y,v;\theta^{\star})=C_{\mathrm{res}}^{-1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v-\mathbb{E}[v]-C_{\widehat{v},\widehat{y}}C_{\widehat{y},\widehat{y}}^{-1}\bigl(y-\mathbb{E}[y]\bigr)}}\right).

Its inverse, evaluated at the observation
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
, is

S(y^{\dagger}_{j+1},\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})^{-1}(z)=C_{\mathrm{res}}^{1/2}z+\mathbb{E}[v]+C_{\widehat{v},\widehat{y}}C_{\widehat{y},\widehat{y}}^{-1}\bigl(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-\mathbb{E}[y]\bigr).

Composing these maps cancels

C_{\mathrm{res}}^{1/2}
with

C_{\mathrm{res}}^{-1/2}
, as well as the mean terms, and gives

T(v,y,y^{\dagger}_{j+1})=v+C_{\widehat{v},\widehat{y}}C_{\widehat{y},\widehat{y}}^{-1}(y^{\dagger}_{j+1}-y).

∎

10.4.4
Amortized Smoothing

In this subsection, we generalize the approach in Subsection
9.3.3
by seeking the transport map
T
that explicitly depends on the observation and pushes forward a distribution
\pi_{j}(v_{j})=\mathbb{P}(v_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})
for the state at time
j
to the smoothing distribution
\mathbb{P}(v_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j},y^{\dagger}_{j+1})
given a new observation
y^{\dagger}_{j+1}
at time
j+1
.

Remark 10.25
.

The distribution for the state given an observation at one time step in the future is often referred to as the lag-1 smoothing distribution. It is also an essential part of the construction of the optimal
particle filter; see (
6.54
) in Section
6.3.8
. That is, we would like to find map parameters
\theta_{j}\in\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that

(10.32)

T_{j}(v_{j},y^{\dagger}_{j+1};\theta_{j})\sim\mathbb{P}(v_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j},y^{\dagger}_{j+1})=:\pi^{\mathrm{OPF}}_{j},\quad v_{j}\sim\pi_{j}.

∎

We will describe a similar methodology to Subsection
10.4.2
that identifies the map by minimizing a strictly proper scoring rule. The main difference is that we will identify the transport that matches the smoothing distribution
\pi^{\mathrm{OPF}}_{j}
at time
j
rather than the filtering distribution
\pi_{j+1}
. We start by defining the joint distribution

(10.33)

\gamma^{1}_{j}(v_{j},y_{j+1})\coloneqq\mathbb{P}(v_{j},y_{j+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{j})=\int\pi_{j}(v_{j})\mathbb{P}(\widehat{v}_{j+1}|v_{j})\mathsf{l}(y_{j+1}|\widehat{v}_{j+1})d\widehat{v}_{j+1}.

We now introduce the following theorem, which will motivate our choice of objective function.

Theorem 10.26
.

Consider a strictly proper scoring rule
\mathsf{S}(\cdot,\cdot):\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}.
Let
\pi^{\mathrm{OPF}}_{j}(v_{j}|y_{j+1})=\mathbb{P}(v_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j},y_{j+1})
be the lag-1 smoothing distribution
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
given an observation
y_{j+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
at time
j+1
and let
q(\cdot\,;y_{j+1})
denote any distribution
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
parameterized by
y_{j+1}.
Then,

(10.34)

\mathbb{E}^{(v_{j},y_{j+1})\sim\gamma^{1}_{j}}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}(q(\cdot\,;y_{j+1}),v_{j})\Bigr]\geq\mathbb{E}^{(v_{j},y_{j+1})\sim\gamma^{1}_{j}}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}(\pi^{\mathrm{OPF}}_{j}(\cdot|y_{j+1}),v_{j})\Bigr].

Furthermore, equality is achieved if
q(\cdot\,;y_{j+1})=\pi^{\mathrm{OPF}}_{j}(\cdot|y_{j+1})
for
\kappa_{j+1}
-almost every
y_{j+1}
, where
\kappa_{j+1}
denotes the
y_{j+1}
-marginal of
\gamma_{j}^{1}
.

Proof.

First, note that
\gamma^{1}_{j}(v_{j},y_{j+1})=\kappa_{j+1}(y_{j+1})\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j},y_{j+1})
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(v_{j},y_{j+1})\sim\gamma^{1}_{j}}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}(q(\cdot\,;y_{j+1}),v_{j})\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{y_{j+1}\sim\kappa_{j+1}}\mathbb{E}^{v_{j}\sim\pi^{\mathrm{OPF}}_{j}(\cdot|y_{j+1})}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}(q(\cdot\,;y_{j+1}),v_{j})\Bigr].

The result then follows similarly to the proof of Theorem
5.20
.
∎

Based on this theorem, to identify the transport
T
parameterized by
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we introduce the objective function for a strictly proper scoring rule
\mathsf{S}
:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}_{j}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{(v_{j},y_{j+1})\sim\gamma^{1}_{j}}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}(T_{j}(\cdot,y_{j+1};\theta)_{\sharp}\pi_{j},v_{j})\Bigr].

We generalize Data Assumption
10.17
, from Subsection
10.4.2
, and assume access to the following data pairs for the state at time
j
and the observation at time
j+1
to implement this objective. This data structure is integral
to learning the lag-
1
smoothing distribution.

Data Assumption 10.27
.

We are given pairs of i.i.d. samples
\{(v_{j}^{(n)},{\widehat{y}}_{j+1}^{(n)})\}_{n=1}^{N}
from the joint conditional distribution
\gamma_{j}^{1}
.

Given a collection of analysis samples
\{v_{j}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}\sim\pi_{j}
, we can generate the pairs required in Data Assumption
10.27
by sampling synthetic observations using the dynamics and observation model (
6.1
), (
9.1
). That is,

(10.35a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j}^{(n)})+\xi_{j}^{(n)},\quad\xi_{j}^{(n)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma),

(10.35b)

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=h(\widehat{v}_{j+1}^{(n)})+\eta_{j+1}^{(n)},\quad\eta_{j+1}^{(n)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Gamma).

We can then define the following empirical optimization problem for the map parameters:

(10.36a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{N}_{j}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathsf{S}(\pi^{\mathrm{OPF},N}_{j}(\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)};\theta),v_{j}^{(n)}),

(10.36b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}_{j}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}^{N}_{j}(\theta),

where
\pi^{\mathrm{OPF},N}_{j}

represents an empirical measure for the pushforward samples, which depends on the map parameters and an observation
\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)}
. That is,

\pi^{\mathrm{OPF},N}_{j}(\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)};\theta)=\frac{1}{N-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m=1\\
m\neq n\end{subarray}}^{N}\delta_{T_{j}(v_{j}^{(m)},\widehat{y}_{j+1}^{(n)};\theta)}.

The objective may then be implemented via a scoring rule that admits evaluation on empirical measures, as in Example
10.21
.

Remark 10.28
.

The procedure in Subsection
9.2.4
for sampling from the lag-1 smoothing distribution
requires a closed-form expression for the likelihood weights, because it
uses the optimal particle filter from Subsection
6.3.8
to approximately sample
\mathbb{P}(v_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j+1})
. In the linear–Gaussian observation setting considered there, the required weights are available in closed form.
On the other hand, the approach
in this subsection can be implemented for general nonlinear observation
operators, as long as we can sample from the dynamics and
observation model in (
10.35
).
∎

10.5
Bibliography

In addition to the approaches outlined in Section
9.5
for learning ensemble filters and smoothers, the concept of learning from different realizations of the observed data to amortize the cost of inference has become popular in various time-series models. Starting from the variational auto-encoding framework
[
282
]
that operationalized amortization in the machine learning community, neural architectures specifically for nonlinear filtering and smoothing via amortized variational inference were considered in
[
290
,
273
]
. Applications of this approach to hierarchical decision-making and model-based reinforcement learning were explored in
[
54
,
217
]
. In addition, the related neural process framework in
[
194
,
195
]
learns to map sets of time-series observations to variational distributions for probabilistic inference after training on a family of stochastic processes. Amortizing 3DVar was considered in
[
275
]
. Amortizing 4DVar was considered in
[
173
,
522
]
.

Several probabilistic approaches based on transportation of measure have been recently developed for learning filters. These procedures often generalize classical Kalman approaches and reduce their bias when applied to non-Gaussian problems. In particular, the paper
[
446
]
introduces the approach of composing triangular transports as in Theorem
10.22
to build prior-to-posterior maps that correspond to the mean-field EnKF
[
102
]
with linear maps and generalize the EnKF with nonlinear maps.
The concept of mean-field EnKF is overviewed in
[
94
]
and analyzed, in terms of
error in relation to the true filter, in
[
102
]
.

This framework was extended to smoothing in
[
400
]
. Ensemble filtering methods that parameterize the map using optimal transport were considered in
[
13
]
, while using a strictly proper scoring rule cost function to learn the map was explored in
[
36
,
516
]
. In particular
[
36
]
contains analysis justifying
the invocation of the ergodic hypothesis in Subsection
10.3.1
.

For work on learning controllers, a subject closely related
to learning data assimilation algorithms in the presence
of model error, we refer the reader to
[
169
,
141
]
.
Lastly, we note that use of reinforcement learning within data assimilation is an area likely to
see considerable growth; see
[
351
,
224
]
.

We conclude with some remarks on implementation issues that may arise when solving the optimization problems in this chapter. In order to evaluate the variational inference objective
{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)
in Section
10.2
, the expectations can be replaced by empirical means. Additionally, the KL divergence must be computed. In some cases, such as with Gaussian densities, a closed form of the divergence is available: see Example
11.40
, and
identity (
11.18
) in particular. Indeed Gaussian approximation
is, in general, popular both because of the interpretability of the mean
and covariance, and because of the closed form for the divergence. When only samples from
q_{\theta}^{Y^{\dagger}}
are available (such as in an ensemble smoother), the divergence may be approximated by first applying density estimation to these samples. Finally, with all of the considered learning problems, the derivatives of
{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)
will generally be hard to obtain analytically. In this case, auto-differentiation can be applied, and then gradient-based methods used to minimize it. An example of using auto-differentiation in a similar context is provided in
[
312
]
.

Part III
Fundamentals

Chapter 11
Metrics, Divergences, and Scoring Rules

In this chapter we introduce various metrics and divergences to quantify closeness between probability measures in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the set of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We also introduce scoring rules, which quantify
closeness of a probability measure in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to a point in the underlying sample space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
and deterministic scoring rules, which quantify the discrepancy between two points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
To be consistent with the rest of the book the presentation focuses mainly on probability density functions; but the definitions can all be extended to general probability measures. Indeed we will employ Dirac measures in several instances. We start with metrics in Section
11.1
; we continue with
divergences in Section
11.2
; and finally we consider scoring rules in Section
11.3
. Throughout the chapter we will make connections between metrics, divergences and scoring rules; these connections
are summarized in Figure
11.1
.

Our motivation for studying this topic is twofold.
First, some metrics and divergences provide a language in which to
state theoretical results about inverse problems and data assimilation. Second,
other metrics and divergences are used to define loss functions for probabilistic machine learning models based on data, and to design and assess the convergence of machine learning algorithms. As an example of the first motivation, the Hellinger distance is useful for stating stability results for measures; see, for instance, Theorems
1.23
and
1.35
. As an example of the second, the energy distance is a metric that is used as a loss function for tasks such as learning
the prior to posterior map (Section
4.3
) and probabilistic filters (Section
9.3
).
The Kullback–Leibler divergence is
useful both for stating theoretical results for inverse problems and data assimilation (see discussion in bibliography Section
1.7
) and for defining loss functions in machine learning (see Chapter
4
). Scoring rules have traditionally been used for evaluating probabilistic forecasts against samples and hold potential to define objectives for machine learning tasks.

In this chapter, when comparing two probability density functions, we will generically denote them by
\rho
and
\varrho
. When considering scalar-valued random variables we denote the cumulative distribution functions associated with these two probability density functions by
F_{\rho}
and
F_{\varrho}
. Whenever a metric, divergence, or score involves moments or other expectations, we tacitly restrict attention to probability measures for which the displayed quantities are finite.

Metrics
Divergences

Expected scoring rules

Figure 11.1:
Diagram representing relationships between three different ways of quantifying closeness between probability measures.
Metrics impose more restrictive conditions than divergences: only some divergences are metrics. Furthermore, a subset of expected scoring rules—those based on strictly proper scoring rules—lead to divergences,
and in some cases to metrics.

11.1
Metrics

11.1.1
Metrics on the Space of Probability Measures

A
metric
between probability measures is a function
\mathsf{D}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that satisfies the following four properties for all
\rho,\varrho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
:

1.

Non-negative:
\mathsf{D}(\rho,\varrho)\geq 0.

2.

Positive:
\mathsf{D}(\rho,\varrho)=0
if and only if
\rho=\varrho.

3.

Symmetric:
\mathsf{D}(\rho,\varrho)=\mathsf{D}(\varrho,\rho).

4.

Triangle inequality:

1
1

1

Satisfaction of the triangle inequality is also referred to as
sub-additivity
.

\mathsf{D}(\rho,\varrho)\leq\mathsf{D}(\rho,\pi)+\mathsf{D}(\pi,\varrho)
for all
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

A metric is also sometimes referred to as a
distance
.

11.1.2
Total Variation and Hellinger Metrics

Definition 11.1
.

The
total variation distance
between two probability density functions
\rho
and
\varrho
in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined by

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\varrho)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\rho(u)-\varrho(u)|\,du=\frac{1}{2}\|\rho-\varrho\|_{L^{1}}.

The
Hellinger distance
between two probability density functions
\rho
and
\varrho
is defined by

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\rho,\varrho)\coloneqq\biggl(\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Bigl|\sqrt{\rho(u)}-\sqrt{\varrho(u)}\Bigr|^{2}\,du\biggr)^{1/2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\|\sqrt{\rho}-\sqrt{\varrho}\|_{L^{2}}.

∎

We now establish bounds between the Hellinger and total variation distance.

Lemma 11.2
.

For any probability density functions
\rho
and
\varrho
in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
the following hold.

•

(i) The Hellinger metric satisfies

(11.1)

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\rho,\varrho)^{2}=1-\big\langle\sqrt{\rho},\sqrt{\varrho}\big\rangle_{L^{2}}.

•

(ii) The total variation and Hellinger metrics are uniformly bounded; indeed

0\leq\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\varrho)\leq 1,\quad 0\leq\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\rho,\varrho)\leq 1.

•

(iii) The total variation and Hellinger metrics bound one another; indeed

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\varrho)\leq\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\rho,\varrho)\leq\sqrt{\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\varrho)}.

Proof.

Part(i) follows from the fact that all densities are non-negative and have norm one in
L^{1}
.
For part (ii) we note that the lower bounds follow immediately from the
definitions, so we only need to prove the upper bounds. For total variation distance

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\varrho)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\rho(u)-\varrho(u)|\,du\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(u)\,du+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho(u)\,du=1.

For the Hellinger distance the upper bound follows from (
11.1
).

For the lower bound in part (iii) we use the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\varrho)=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big|\sqrt{\rho(u)}-\sqrt{\varrho(u)}\Big|\Big|\sqrt{\rho(u)}+\sqrt{\varrho(u)}\Big|\,du

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \biggl(\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big|\sqrt{\rho(u)}-\sqrt{\varrho(u)}\Big|^{2}\,du\biggr)^{1/2}\biggl(\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big|\sqrt{\rho(u)}+\sqrt{\varrho(u)}\Big|^{2}\,du\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\rho,\varrho)\biggl(\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big(2\rho(u)+2\varrho(u)\big)\,du\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sqrt{2}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\rho,\varrho).

For the upper bound in part (iii) first notice that

|\sqrt{\rho(u)}-\sqrt{\varrho(u)}|\leq|\sqrt{\rho(u)}+\sqrt{\varrho(u)}|

since
\sqrt{\rho(u)},\sqrt{\varrho(u)}\geq 0
. Thus we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\rho,\varrho)=

\displaystyle\ \biggl(\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big|\sqrt{\rho(u)}-\sqrt{\varrho(u)}\Big|^{2}\,du\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \biggl(\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big|\sqrt{\rho(u)}-\sqrt{\varrho(u)}\Big|\Big|\sqrt{\rho(u)}+\sqrt{\varrho(u)}\Big|\,du\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \biggl(\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|\rho(u)-\varrho(u)\big|\,du\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sqrt{\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\varrho)}.

∎

We now show that, if two densities are close in total variation or in Hellinger distance, then expectations computed with respect to both densities are also close. In addition, the following lemma also provides a useful characterization of the total variation distance.

Lemma 11.3
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}.

•

(i) Assume furthermore that
f
satisfies

|f|_{\infty}:=\sup_{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(u)|<\infty.
It holds that

\bigl|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\rho}[f]-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\varrho}[f]\bigr|\leq 2|f|_{\infty}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\varrho).

In fact, the following variational characterization of the total variation distance holds:

(11.2)

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\varrho)=\frac{1}{2}\sup_{|f|_{\infty}\leq 1}\bigl|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\rho}[f]-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\varrho}[f]\bigr|.

•

(ii) If, instead,
f
is such that

f_{2}:=\bigl(\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\rho}[|f|^{2}]+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\varrho}[|f|^{2}]\bigr)^{1/2}<\infty
, then

\big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\rho}[f]-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\varrho}[f]\big|\leq 2f_{2}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\rho,\varrho).

Proof.

For part (i) we start by noting that

\displaystyle\bigl|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\rho}[f]-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\varrho}[f]\bigr|=

\displaystyle\ \Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(u)\bigl(\rho(u)-\varrho(u)\bigr)\,du\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ |f|_{\infty}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\rho(u)-\varrho(u)|\,du

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ 2|f|_{\infty}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\varrho).

Thus, for any
f
with
|f|_{\infty}=1
, we obtain

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\varrho)\geq\frac{1}{2}\big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\rho}[f]-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\varrho}[f]\big|.

We complete the proof of part (i) by exhibiting a choice of
f
with
|f|_{\infty}=1
that achieves equality. To this end we choose
f(u):=\text{sign}\Bigl(\rho(u)-\varrho(u)\Bigr)
, so that
f(u)\Bigl(\rho(u)-\varrho(u)\Bigr)=|\rho(u)-\varrho(u)|.
Then
|f|_{\infty}=1,
and

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\varrho)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\rho(u)-\varrho(u)|\,du

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(u)\Bigl(\rho(u)-\varrho(u)\Bigr)\,du

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bigl|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\rho}[f]-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\varrho}[f]\bigr|.

For part (ii) we use the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality
to show that

\displaystyle\bigl|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\rho}[f]-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\varrho}[f]\bigr|=

\displaystyle\ \biggl|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(u)\Bigl(\sqrt{\rho(u)}-\sqrt{\varrho(u)}\Bigr)\Bigl(\sqrt{\rho(u)}+\sqrt{\varrho(u)}\,\Bigr)\,du\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \biggl(\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big|\sqrt{\rho(u)}-\sqrt{\varrho(u)}\Big|^{2}\,du\biggr)^{1/2}\biggl(2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(u)|^{2}\Big|\sqrt{\rho(u)}+\sqrt{\varrho(u)}\Big|^{2}\,du\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\rho,\varrho)\Big(4\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(u)|^{2}\bigl(\rho(u)+\varrho(u)\bigr)\,du\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ 2f_{2}\,\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\rho,\varrho).

∎

Remark 11.4
.

When working with general probability measures, not necessarily restricted to measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with Lebesgue density,
the characterization (
11.2
) should be taken as the
definition
of total variation distance.
Definition
11.1
then exhibits a
property
of the total variation distance when the measures are
defined over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and possess Lebesgue densities.
∎

Example 11.5
.

Consider the two Gaussians
\rho=\mathcal{N}(m_{\rho},\Sigma_{\rho})
and
\varrho=\mathcal{N}(m_{\varrho},\Sigma_{\varrho}).

Using these Gaussians we present examples demonstrating the different manner in which total variation distance behaves,
depending on properties of means and covariances.

First consider the case where

\Sigma_{\varrho}=0
, so that
\varrho
is a Dirac measure at
m_{\varrho}
. Then, if
\Sigma_{\rho}>0
, it
follows that
\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\varrho)=1
; we may show this using the characterization (
11.2
),
and the choice of test functions

f_{\epsilon}=1-2\mathbb{1}_{B(m_{\varrho},\epsilon)}.

To see this note that then
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\varrho}[f_{\epsilon}]=-1
. On the other hand
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\rho}[f_{\epsilon}]\to 1
as
\epsilon\to 0.

Taking the limit
\epsilon\to 0
delivers the desired supremum.

Now consider the case where
\Sigma_{\varrho}=\Sigma_{\rho}=0
.
Then
\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\varrho)=0
if
m_{\rho}=m_{\varrho}
; however if

m_{\rho}\neq m_{\varrho}
then
\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\varrho)=1
a fact which can be seen
by again using characterization (
11.2
)
and now making choice of test function equal to the difference of two indicator functions with disjoint support, and supported on each of

m_{\rho}
and
m_{\varrho}.

∎

Remark 11.6
.

Consider the setting where
\rho
and
\varrho
are probability density functions. Then the Hellinger distance between
\rho
and
\varrho
is equal to one if and only if the measures have disjoint supports. This follows from (
11.1
): the Hellinger distance is
1
if and only if
\sqrt{\rho}\sqrt{\varrho}=0
in a Lebesgue a.e. sense, since the square-roots of the densities are non-negative functions; this implies that their supports must be disjoint. A similar result holds for total variation distance. More generally,
for probability measures that may include atoms, both statements are
most naturally expressed in terms of mutual singularity:

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\rho,\varrho)=1
if and only if
\rho
and
\varrho
are
mutually singular, and the same equivalence holds for

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\varrho)=1
. Example
11.5
illustrates this issue.

∎

11.1.3
Transportation Metrics

We start by defining the notions of
coupling
and
transport map
.

Definition 11.7
.

Let
\rho,

\varrho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).
A
coupling
of
\rho
and
\varrho
is
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
with the property that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\pi(z,u)\,du=\rho(z),\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\pi(z,u)\,dz=\varrho(u);

thus the
z
and
u
marginals of
\pi
deliver
\rho
and
\varrho
respectively.
We denote the set of all such couplings by
\Pi_{\rho,\varrho}.

∎

Definition 11.8
.

Let
\rho,

\varrho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).
A
transport map

g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
between
\rho
and
\varrho
is
a map with the property that
\varrho=g_{\sharp}\rho.

∎

We now link these two concepts through two distinct formulations of

optimal transport
.

Kantorovich Formulation

Given a cost function
\mathsf{c}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
we
define the Kantorovich formulation of the
optimal transport
problem as follows:

(11.3)

\displaystyle\pi^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\arginf_{\pi\in\Pi_{\rho,\varrho}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{c}(z,u)\pi(z,u)\,dzdu.

The reason for the “optimal” terminology is clear from the infimization.
The connection of optimal transport to transport maps on
\mathbb{R}^{d}

is made clear in the Monge formulation of optimal
transport which we now introduce.

Monge Formulation

In this formulation we explicitly link
\varrho
with a pushforward of
\rho
through a transport map
g
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We do this by identifying the map which solves the following minimization problem:

(11.4)

\displaystyle g^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\arginf_{g:g_{\sharp}\rho=\varrho}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{c}\bigl(z,g(z)\bigr)\rho(z)\,dz.

In the following
|\cdot|_{r}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
is the norm defined by
|u|_{r}^{r}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}|u_{j}|^{r}.

Under certain smoothness assumptions, the Kantorovich formulation
has solution within the Monge class; see the bibliography
Section
11.4
for detailed discussion of this topic. We present a specific setting in
which such a result holds:

Theorem 11.9
.

Assume that
\mathsf{c}\bigl(z,u\bigr)=|u-z|_{r}^{r}
for some
r\in(1,\infty)
and that
\rho\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
is a probability density function.
Assume furthermore that
\mathbb{E}^{z\sim\rho}|z|_{r}^{r},\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\varrho}|u|_{r}^{r}<\infty.
Then the
Monge pushforward optimal transport problem has a unique solution
g^{\star}.
Furthermore
the optimal Kantorovich coupling is also unique and is
constructed using the Monge pushforward:

(11.5a)

\displaystyle\pi^{\star}

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{id},g^{\star})_{\sharp}\rho,

(11.5b)

\displaystyle\pi^{\star}(z,u)

\displaystyle=\rho(z)\delta\bigl(u-{g^{\star}(z)}\bigr).

Metrics From Optimal Transport

The notion of optimal transport may be used to define
families of probability metrics by choosing the cost function in
the Kantorovich formulation of optimal transport
to be a power of a metric on
\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Definition 11.10
.

Given a metric
\mathsf{d}(\cdot,\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
p\geq 1,
the
Wasserstein
-p
distance
between two probability density functions
\rho
and
\varrho
is defined by

(11.6)

\displaystyle W_{p}(\rho,\varrho)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\biggl(\inf_{\pi\in\Pi_{\rho,\varrho}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{d}(z,u)^{p}\pi(z,u)\,dz\,du\biggr)^{1/p}.

∎

Assume that there is a reference point
u_{0}
such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{d}(u,u_{0})^{p}\rho(u)du<\infty
; by
the triangle inequality for
\mathsf{d}(\cdot,\cdot)
, the existence of one such point
u_{0}
implies that the result holds
for any choice of
u_{0}.
We say, then, that
\rho
has finite
p
-th moments. The Wasserstein
-p
distance
W_{p}
defined above
is indeed a metric in the space of probability measures with finite
p
-th moments.
The metric has two key aspects: first, it derives from an underlying metric on

\mathbb{R}^{d}
; and second, it allows for a meaningful distance to
be calculated between measures which are mutually singular.

Example 11.11
.

Recall Example
11.5
and again consider the Gaussians

\rho=\mathcal{N}(m_{\rho},\Sigma_{\rho})
and
\varrho=\mathcal{N}(m_{\varrho},\Sigma_{\varrho}).

It may be shown that, if the metric
\mathsf{d}
induced by the Euclidean
norm
|\cdot|
is used, then

W_{2}(\rho,\varrho)^{2}=|m_{\rho}-m_{\varrho}|^{2}+{\rm Tr}\Bigl(\Sigma_{\rho}+\Sigma_{\varrho}-2\bigl(\Sigma_{\rho}^{1/2}\Sigma_{\varrho}\Sigma_{\rho}^{1/2}\bigr)^{1/2}\Bigr).

In particular, if
\varrho
is a Dirac measure at
m_{\varrho}
then

W_{2}(\rho,\varrho)^{2}=|m_{\rho}-m_{\varrho}|^{2}+{\rm Tr}\bigl(\Sigma_{\rho}\bigr).

Thus, in the Wasserstein
-2
distance, Gaussian
\rho
is close to
a Dirac at
m_{\varrho}
if the mean of the Gaussian
\rho
is close
to
m_{\varrho}
and if its covariance is small. In contrast, Example
11.5

shows that the total variation distance
between Gaussian
\rho
and a Dirac at
m_{\varrho}
is maximal, and equal to
1
, unless the two measures coincide (
m_{\rho}=m_{\varrho}
,
\Sigma_{\rho}=0
) in which case it is
0.
Notice also that if
\rho
and
\varrho
are Diracs at
m_{\rho}
and
m_{\varrho}
, then
W_{2}(\rho,\varrho)=|m_{\rho}-m_{\varrho}|;
thus closeness of the mass locations
m_{\rho}
and
m_{\varrho}
in Euclidean space translates into closeness of
\rho
and
\varrho
in Wasserstein distance.
Again, Example
11.5
shows that two Diracs are maximally far apart in the total variation distance unless they coincide.
∎

One-Dimensional Setting

Optimal transport metrics have as an advantage that they
directly link a metric on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to the metric on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).
However it is an implicit definition, via an optimization.
Notwithstanding the explicit Gaussian formula in Example

11.11
and the Monge formulation via a map on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
it can be hard to develop intuition about the metric in general.
In one dimension, however, there are a number of explicit formulae for the optimal transport metric which are insightful and build intuition; we describe them here.

Lemma 11.12
.

Let
\rho,\varrho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})
have invertible
cumulative distribution functions
F_{\rho},F_{\varrho}\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow[0,1]
, respectively. Then, for
p\geq 1
, the Wasserstein-
p
distance with metric
\mathsf{d}
induced by the Euclidean norm
|\cdot|
has the form

W_{p}(\rho,\varrho)^{p}=\int_{0}^{1}|F_{\rho}^{-1}(q)-F_{\varrho}^{-1}(q)|^{p}\,dq.

Proof.

Recall that the pushforward of a random variable under its own cumulative distribution
function
F
is a uniform random variable on
[0,1].

Moreover, applying the (generalized) inverse of
F
to a uniform random variable on
[0,1]
produces a random variable with cumulative distribution function
F
. As a consequence,
the map
g(z)=F_{\varrho}^{-1}\circ F_{\rho}(z)
pushes forward
\rho
to
\varrho
. Furthermore, this pushforward
leads to the optimal Kantorovich coupling in the one-dimensional setting; this follows from the Monge formulation as detailed in
the bibliography Section
11.4
.
By substituting this map in the objective and performing the change of variables
q=F_{\rho}(z)
we have,
using
dq=F_{\rho}^{\prime}(z)\,dz=\rho(z)\,dz
,

\displaystyle W_{p}(\rho,\varrho)^{p}

\displaystyle=\inf_{g_{\sharp}\rho=\varrho}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|z-g(z)|^{p}\rho(z)\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|z-F_{\varrho}^{-1}\circ F_{\rho}(z)|^{p}\rho(z)\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}|F_{\rho}^{-1}(q)-F_{\varrho}^{-1}(q)|^{p}\,dq.

∎

The Wasserstein-
p
distance for
p=1
has some additional computational advantages. It is an integral probability metric (IPM), a concept introduced in Subsection
11.1.4
below; see Example
11.17
.
Furthermore, the closed-form expression in one dimension can be rewritten without requiring the inverse of cumulative distribution functions:

Lemma 11.13
.

In the setting of Lemma
11.12
,
the Wasserstein-
1
distance can be expressed as

W_{1}(\rho,\varrho)=\int_{0}^{1}|F_{\rho}^{-1}(q)-F_{\varrho}^{-1}(q)|\,dq=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|F_{\rho}(z)-F_{\varrho}(z)|\,dz.

Proof.

The idea behind the proof of this result is shown in Figure
11.2
. It illustrates that integrating the difference between the cumulative distribution functions vertically (left) is equivalent to the horizontal integration (right).
∎

Figure 11.2:
Integration of the difference between cumulative distribution functions (left) and their inverses (right)

Sliced Wasserstein Distance

The fact that the Wasserstein distance in
\mathbb{R}

is explicit, and defined by quadrature,
may be used as a building block to construct a computable metric in
\mathbb{R}^{d}.

The
sliced Wasserstein distance
is a readily
computable distance that avoids explicit solution of an optimal transport problem and is defined by

{SW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}{W}_{p}^{p}(P^{\theta}_{\sharp}\mu,P^{\theta}_{\sharp}\nu)\,d\theta,\quad P^{\theta}u=\langle u,\theta\rangle.

The integral over the unit sphere may be approximated by Monte Carlo sampling leading to an implementable methodology based on computation of multiple one-dimensional Wasserstein distances which, in turn, may be approximated by quadrature.

11.1.4
Integral Probability Metrics

We now build on the variational characterization (
11.2
)
of the total variation distance
to describe a wide class of probability metrics.
Rather than taking the supremum over functions with a bound on their maximum value,
we consider taking supremum over other classes of functions. To this end,
let
\mathcal{F}
be a set of real-valued functions

f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
known as discriminators.
These are used to define a metric as follows:

Definition 11.14
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a set of discriminator functions. An
integral probability metric

(IPM)
\mathsf{D}_{\mathcal{F}}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined by setting, for measures

\rho,\varrho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

(11.7)

\mathsf{D}_{\mathcal{F}}(\rho,\varrho)\coloneqq\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\,\big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\rho}[f]-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{\varrho}[f]\big|.

∎

It is natural to ask, then, under what conditions on
\mathcal{F}
the preceding definition
yields a metric. Citation to proof of the following may be found in the bibliography Section
11.4
.

Lemma 11.15
.

Function
\mathsf{D}_{\mathcal{F}}

given by (
11.7
) defines a metric if and only if

\mathcal{F}
separates points on the space of probability measures: for any two different probability measures

\rho,\varrho
there is an element in
\mathcal{F}
with
different expectations under
\rho
and
\varrho.

Example 11.16
.

The total variation distance, given in (
11.2
), corresponds to choosing

\mathcal{F}
to be the scaled unit ball
of functions bounded by
\frac{1}{2}
in
L^{\infty}
. Furthermore, the idea of separating points in the space of probability measures
is central to the calculations concerning total variation distance in Example
11.5
.
∎

Example 11.17
.

A second important example of an IPM is the Wasserstein-
1
distance. This metric is reproduced as an
IPM by choosing
\mathcal{F}
to be the
space of Lipschitz continuous functions with Lipschitz constant less than or equal to one; see the bibliography Section
11.4
.
∎

Example 11.18
.

In the following subsection we introduce the concept of RKHS.
A third example of an IPM arises when
\mathcal{F}
is the unit ball of an RKHS.
The resulting metric, known as the maximum mean discrepancy, will also be described
in the following subsection.
∎

11.1.5
Maximum Mean Discrepancy and Energy Distance

We start by defining the concept of kernel, in which we employ the notation
\mathbf{1}
to denote the vector of all ones:

Definition 11.19
.

Let
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}.
A
kernel

is a function
c:D\times D\to\mathbb{R}
.

It is called
symmetric

if
c(u,u^{\prime})=c(u^{\prime},u)
for all
(u,u^{\prime})\in D\times D.
Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
U=\{u^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N}\subset D
. Define the
Gram matrix

C\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
by its entries

C_{ij}=c\bigl(u^{(i)},u^{(j)}\bigr).
A symmetric kernel is said to be
positive definite

if, for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
U\in D^{\otimes N}
,
C\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}_{\textrm{sym},\geq}.

If matrix
C\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}_{\textrm{sym},>}
the kernel is called
strictly
positive definite. If, for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
U=\{u^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N}\subset D
,
matrix
C
has the property that, for all
v
such that
\langle v,\mathbf{1}\rangle=0
,
\langle v,Cv\rangle\leq 0
, the kernel is called
negative definite
.
∎

Definition 11.20
.

Let
c:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a symmetric and positive definite kernel. The

maximum mean discrepancy
(MMD) with kernel
c
between
\rho
and
\varrho
in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined by

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MMD}}}^{2}(\rho,\varrho)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{(u,u^{\prime})\sim\rho\otimes\rho}\bigl[c(u,u^{\prime})\bigr]+\mathbb{E}^{(v,v^{\prime})\sim\varrho\otimes\varrho}\bigl[c(v,v^{\prime})\bigr]-2\mathbb{E}^{(u,v)\sim\rho\otimes\varrho}\bigl[c(u,v)\bigr].

∎

We will now show that MMD is an example of an IPM. To that end, we recall first the notion of an RKHS:

Definition 11.21
.

A Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
of functions
f:D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is called a
reproducing kernel Hilbert space
(RKHS) if there is a function
c:D\times D\to\mathbb{R}
, with
c(u,\cdot)\in\mathcal{H}
for all
u\in D
, and with the property that, for all
f\in\mathcal{H}
and
u\in D,

(11.8)

f(u)=\langle f,c(u,\cdot)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.

This is known as the
reproducing property
and
the function
c
is called the
reproducing kernel
.
∎

An important classical result, the Moore–Aronszajn theorem, ensures that given a symmetric and positive definite kernel
c:D\times D\to\mathbb{R}
, there is a unique Hilbert space of functions on
D
for which
c
is a reproducing kernel. Citation to this result can be found in the bibliography Section
11.4
.

Lemma 11.22
.

Let
c:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a symmetric and positive definite kernel. Let
\mathcal{H}
be the RKHS with reproducing kernel
c.

Define
\mathcal{F}=\{f\in\mathcal{H}:\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq 1\}.
Then the MMD with kernel
c
is an IPM with set of discriminator functions
\mathcal{F}.

Proof.

Let
m_{\rho}(\cdot):=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{u\sim\rho}[c(u,\cdot)]

denote the mean embedding of a distribution
\rho
with respect to the kernel
c.

By the reproducing property (
11.8
) for functions
f
in RKHS
\mathcal{H}
we have

\mathbb{E}^{\rho}[f]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(u)\rho(u)\,du=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle f,c(u,\cdot)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\,\rho(u)\,du=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle f,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}c(u,\cdot)\rho(u)\,du}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\langle f,m_{\rho}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.

Using the definition of
\mathcal{F}
, and definition of the Hilbert space norm through duality,
\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}}=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\,\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\,\bigl|\mathbb{E}^{\rho}[f]-\mathbb{E}^{\varrho}[f]\bigr|

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\,\bigl|\langle f,m_{\rho}-m_{\varrho}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\bigr|

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\,\langle f,m_{\rho}-m_{\varrho}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\|m_{\rho}-m_{\varrho}\|_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\langle m_{\rho},m_{\rho}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-2\langle m_{\rho},m_{\varrho}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}+\langle m_{\varrho},m_{\varrho}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\Bigr)^{1/2}.

The right-hand side is identical to
\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MMD}}}(\rho,\varrho).
To see this, we can apply for each term the reproducing property to compute the inner product of the mean embedding.
In particular we have

\displaystyle\langle m_{\rho},m_{\varrho}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\langle\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}[c(u,\cdot)],\mathbb{E}^{u^{\prime}\sim\varrho}[c(u^{\prime},\cdot)]\Bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{(u,u^{\prime})\sim\rho\otimes\varrho}\Bigl[\bigl\langle c(u,\cdot),c(u^{\prime},\cdot)\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\Bigr]

(11.9)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{(u,u^{\prime})\sim\rho\otimes\varrho}\bigl[c(u,u^{\prime})\bigr].

and, similarly,

\langle m_{\rho},m_{\rho}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\mathbb{E}^{(u,u^{\prime})\sim\rho\otimes\rho}\bigl[c(u,u^{\prime})\bigr].

∎

Remark 11.23
.

The reproducing property in (
11.9
) permits the computation of inner products with respect to the kernel (or an associated infinite-dimensional feature map) using evaluations of the kernel alone. In many machine learning tasks, this property is referred to as the
kernel trick.

∎

Definition 11.24
.

The kernel of an RKHS
\mathcal{H}
defined by
c(\cdot,\cdot)

is
characteristic
if the property

\mathbb{E}^{\rho}[f]=\mathbb{E}^{\varrho}[f]
for all

f\in\mathcal{H}
implies that
\rho=\varrho
.
∎

Remark 11.25
.

We have shown that MMD is an IPM with
\mathcal{F}=\{f\in\mathcal{H}:\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq 1\}
given by the unit ball of an RKHS
\mathcal{H}
defined by kernel
c
.
The IPM defined by MMD is a metric if kernel
c
is
characteristic.
∎

Example 11.26
.

Gaussian and Laplace kernels
are examples of characteristic kernels.
∎

We defined MMD for positive definite kernels. The energy distance,
which we define next, is obtained by formally setting

c(u,u^{\prime})=-|u-u^{\prime}|
in the expression defining MMD. Although
c
is
not positive definite, the resulting expression is non-negative
because the Euclidean distance kernel
|u-u^{\prime}|
is negative definite.

Definition 11.27
.

The
energy distance

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm E}}}
between
\rho
and
\varrho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined by

(11.10)

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm E}}}^{2}(\rho,\varrho)\coloneqq 2\mathbb{E}^{(u,v)\sim\rho\otimes\varrho}|u-v|-\mathbb{E}^{(u,u^{\prime})\sim\rho\otimes\rho}|u-u^{\prime}|-\mathbb{E}^{(v,v^{\prime})\sim\varrho\otimes\varrho}|v-v^{\prime}|.

∎

In Subsection
11.3.1
we
discuss energy scores.
There we prove the following lemma, based on
an equivalent formulation of energy distance in terms of
characteristic functions.
See, in particular, the proof of Lemma
11.28
.

Lemma 11.28
.

The energy distance
\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm E}}}
is a metric on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Remark 11.29
.

A key property of the MMD and the energy distance is that they are amenable to
ensemble approximation; they can be implemented using only samples
from
\rho
or
\varrho
. Given independent samples
\{u^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N}\sim\rho
and, independently, given independent samples
\{v^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{M}\sim\varrho,
we define the empirical measures

(11.11)

\displaystyle\rho^{N}(u)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{u^{(n)}}(u),\quad\varrho^{M}(u)=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\delta_{v^{(m)}}(u).

We can estimate the MMD distance between
\rho
and
\varrho
by

(11.12a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MMD}}}^{2}}(\rho,\varrho)\approx{\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MMD}}}^{2}}(\rho^{N},\varrho^{M}),

(11.12b)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MMD}}}^{2}}(\rho^{N},\varrho^{M})=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i,j}c(u^{(i)},u^{(j)})+\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{i,j}c(v^{(i)},v^{(j)})-\frac{2}{NM}\sum_{i,j}c(u^{(i)},v^{(j)}).

Choosing the kernel
c(u,u^{\prime})=-|u-u^{\prime}|
yields the following
estimate for the energy distance between
\rho
and
\varrho
:

(11.13)

{\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm E}}}^{2}}(\rho^{N},\varrho^{M})=\frac{2}{NM}\sum_{i,j}|u^{(i)}-v^{(j)}|-\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i,j}|u^{(i)}-u^{(j)}|-\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{i,j}|v^{(i)}-v^{(j)}|.

∎

Remark 11.30
.

It is standard in practice to use a slightly different empirical approximation of MMD. This
arises from dropping the diagonal term in the first two sums appearing in (
11.12
b),
and renormalizing, resulting in the approximation

{\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MMD}}}^{2}}(\rho,\varrho)\approx\frac{1}{N(N-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}c(u^{(i)},u^{(j)})+\frac{1}{M(M-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}c(v^{(i)},v^{(j)})-\frac{2}{NM}\sum_{i,j}c(u^{(i)},v^{(j)}).

We present the approximation (
11.12
) including the diagonal term because it is
simple conceptually. Dropping the diagonal term, however, leads to an unbiased estimator.
Formula (
11.13
), in particular, can also be modified similarly to obtain an unbiased
estimator of the squared energy distance. Note, however, that these unbiased estimators can be negative
at finite sample size.
∎

Remark 11.31
.

An important property of both the MMD with isotropic kernel and the energy distance is that they are rotation invariant. That is, the
metrics do not change under the transformations

u\leftarrow Ru
and
v\leftarrow Rv
, for any unitary matrix
R
.
∎

11.1.6
Metrics on the Space of Random Probability Measures

In these notes, when we consider random variables, they primarily take values in finite-dimensional
Euclidean space. However it is sometimes useful to consider random variables which take values in the space of probability measures on finite-dimensional Euclidean space. This leads to
consideration of the space of random probability measures. This
space arises naturally when building methodologies from empirical sampling or from Monte Carlo methods.

We consider functions
\pi:\Omega\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
for some abstract probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{B},\mathbb{P});
expectation under
\mathbb{P}

is denoted by
\mathbb{E}.

We say that
\pi:\Omega\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is an element of the space of random probability measures.
For any fixed
\omega\in\Omega
let
\pi=\pi(\omega)
and
define
\mathbb{E}^{\pi}\bigl[f\bigr]\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(u)\pi(du)
.
Given
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
recall that
|f|_{\infty}:=\sup_{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(u)|.

Definition 11.32
.

Let
\pi,\pi^{\prime}:\Omega\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then

\mathsf{d_{rand}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\coloneqq\sup_{|f|_{\infty}\leq 1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}^{\pi}\bigl[f\bigr]-\mathbb{E}^{\pi^{\prime}}\bigl[f\bigr]}}\right)^{2}}}\right]}}\right|^{1/2}.

∎

Remark 11.33
.

Note that
\mathsf{d_{rand}}
reduces to twice the total variation distance (
11.2
)
when
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
are not random.
∎

Given a fixed deterministic probability density function
\pi,
we now define a random Monte Carlo approximation, from independent samples, using Diracs:

(11.14)

\pi^{N}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MC}}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{u^{(n)}},\,\,u^{(n)}\sim\pi\quad{\rm i.i.d.}

Note that
\pi^{N}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MC}}}
is a random probability measure, over space
\Omega
underlying the random samples

\{u^{(n)}\},
approximating
\pi.
From this random probability measure we can compute random approximations of
expectation under
\pi:

(11.15)

\mathbb{E}^{\pi^{N}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MC}}}}\bigl[f\bigr]=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}f(u^{(n)}),\,\,u^{(n)}\sim\pi\quad{\rm i.i.d.}

The metric
\mathsf{d_{rand}}
introduced in Definition
11.32
is well-adapted to quantify the error in approximating the population limit
\pi
by
\pi^{N}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MC}}}.
Let
\mathbb{E}
denote expectation with respect to the
N
i.i.d. samples from
\pi
defining the Monte Carlo approximation of
\pi
, a product measure; likewise
Var
denotes variance under the same product measure. The following theorem captures the properties of this random approximation:

Theorem 11.34
.

The Monte Carlo approximation
\pi^{N}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MC}}}
of
\pi
satisfies the following two properties:

\begin{split}\sup_{|f|_{\infty}\leq 1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathbb{E}^{\pi^{N}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MC}}}}\bigl[f\bigr]-\mathbb{E}^{\pi}\bigl[f\bigr]}}\right]}}}\right|&=0,\\
\mathsf{d_{rand}}(\pi^{N}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MC}}},\pi)^{2}&\leq\frac{1}{N}.\end{split}

Proof.

To prove that the estimator is unbiased, use linearity of the expected value and that
u^{(n)}\sim\pi
to obtain the following identity, from which the desired result follows:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathbb{E}^{\pi^{N}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MC}}}}\bigl[f\bigr]}}\right]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Biggl[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}f\bigl(u^{(n)}\bigr)\Biggr]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}N\mathbb{E}^{\pi}\bigl[f\bigr]=\mathbb{E}^{\pi}\bigl[f\bigr]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[\mathbb{E}^{\pi}\bigl[f\bigr]\bigr].

For the error estimate expressed in terms of
\mathsf{d_{rand}}(\cdot,\cdot)
we note that, since
\mathbb{E}^{\pi^{N}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MC}}}}\bigl[f\bigr]
is unbiased, its variance coincides
with its mean squared error. Using the fact that the
u^{(n)}\sim\pi
are independent we deduce that

\displaystyle\text{Var}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathbb{E}^{\pi^{N}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MC}}}}\bigl[f\bigr]}}\right]

\displaystyle=\text{Var}\Biggl[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}f\bigl(u^{(n)}\bigr)\Biggr]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}N\text{Var}_{\pi}[f]=\frac{1}{N}\text{Var}_{\pi}[f].

Assuming
|f|_{\infty}\leq 1,
we have

\text{Var}_{\pi}[f]=\mathbb{E}^{\pi}\bigl[f^{2}\bigr]-\mathbb{E}^{\pi}\bigl[f\bigr]^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}^{\pi}\bigl[f^{2}\bigr]\leq 1,

and therefore

\sup_{|f|_{\infty}\leq 1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}^{\pi^{N}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MC}}}}\bigl[f\bigr]-\mathbb{E}^{\pi}\bigl[f\bigr]}}\right)^{2}}}\right]}}}\right|=\sup_{|f|_{\infty}\leq 1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\frac{1}{N}\text{Var}_{\pi}[f]}}\right|\leq\frac{1}{N}.

∎

11.2
Divergences

Recall the conditions for a metric given in Section
11.1
. If we remove the conditions of
symmetry and the triangle inequality then we obtain a

statistical divergence
:
a function
\mathsf{D}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that satisfies the following two properties for all
\rho,\varrho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
:

1.

Non-negative:
\mathsf{D}(\rho,\varrho)\geq 0.

2.

Positive:
\mathsf{D}(\rho,\varrho)=0
if and only if
\rho=\varrho.

To emphasize the asymmetry in their arguments, divergences are often written with double bars
\mathsf{D}(\rho\|\varrho)
or a colon
\mathsf{D}(\rho:\varrho)
. We adopt
the former convention. The following is a straightforward consequence of the fact that divergence is obtained by removing two of the four defining characteristics of a metric:

Lemma 11.35
.

Every metric is a statistical divergence.

11.2.1
\mathsf{f}
-Divergences

We now define the special class of
\mathsf{f}
-divergences.
An important feature of this subclass of divergences is that they are defined through expectation of
a ratio of two probability density functions. To simplify the presentation, throughout this
section we restrict our attention to positive densities.

Definition 11.36
.

Let
\mathsf{f}\colon[0,\infty)\rightarrow(-\infty,\infty]
be a convex function that satisfies
\mathsf{f}(0)=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\mathsf{f}(t)
(this value can be
\infty
) and
\mathsf{f}(1)=0
.
The
\mathsf{f}
-divergence
between distributions with positive densities
\rho,\varrho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined by

\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{f}}(\rho\|\varrho)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{\varrho}\Bigl[\mathsf{f}\Bigl(\frac{\rho}{\varrho}\Bigr)\Bigr]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{f}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\rho(u)}{\varrho(u)}}}\right)\varrho(u)\,du.

∎

Throughout, we restrict attention to choices of
\mathsf{f}
for which

\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{f}}(\rho\|\varrho)=0
if and only if

\rho=\varrho,
so that
\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{f}}
is a statistical
divergence in the sense defined above.

Remark 11.37
.

If we changed ‘convex function’ to ‘strictly convex function’ in Definition
11.36
,
then the preceding restriction would be automatic. However total variation would not, then, be an
\mathsf{f}
-divergence.
Hence the reason for why we have formulated things as we have.
∎

Remark 11.38
.

Some
\mathsf{f}
-divergences define metrics. For example, the choice
\mathsf{f}(t)=\frac{1}{2}|t-1|
delivers
\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{f}}(\rho\|\varrho)=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\varrho)
and the choice
\mathsf{f}(t)=(1-\sqrt{t})^{2}

delivers
\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{f}}(\rho\|\varrho)=2\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\rho,\varrho)^{2}.
It can be shown that the only
\mathsf{f}
-divergence that is also an IPM is the total variation distance.
∎

The next definition introduces two important
\mathsf{f}
-divergences that do not define metrics: the
Kullback–Leibler divergence and the
\chi^{2}

divergence. They correspond to choosing
\mathsf{f}(t)=t\log t
and
\mathsf{f}(t)=(t-1)^{2},
respectively.
Reversing the order in the Kullback–Leibler divergence is achieved by setting
\mathsf{f}(t)=-\log t.

Definition 11.39
.

The
Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence
between two positive probability density functions
\rho
and
\varrho
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is defined by

(11.16)

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\rho\|\varrho)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\biggl(\frac{\rho(u)}{\varrho(u)}\biggr)\rho(u)\,du.

The
\chi^{2}
divergence
between two positive probability density functions
\rho
and
\varrho
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is defined by

(11.17)

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{$\chi^{2}$}}}(\rho\|\varrho)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\rho(u)}{\varrho(u)}-1}}\right)^{2}\,\varrho(u)\,du.

∎

Example 11.40
.

The KL and
\chi^{2}

divergences
can be explicitly computed for
d
-dimensional multivariate Gaussians. Indeed, for

\rho=\mathcal{N}(m_{\rho},\Sigma_{\rho})
and
\varrho=\mathcal{N}(m_{\varrho},\Sigma_{\varrho})
we have:

(11.18a)

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\rho\|\varrho)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\text{Tr}(\Sigma_{\varrho}^{-1}\Sigma_{\rho})-d+(m_{\rho}-m_{\varrho})^{\top}\Sigma_{\varrho}^{-1}(m_{\rho}-m_{\varrho})+\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\det\Sigma_{\varrho}}{\det\Sigma_{\rho}}}}\right)}}\right),

(11.18b)

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{$\chi^{2}$}}}(\rho\|\varrho)

\displaystyle=\frac{\det(\Sigma_{\varrho})}{\sqrt{\det(\Sigma_{\rho})\det(2\Sigma_{\varrho}-\Sigma_{\rho})}}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((m_{\rho}-m_{\varrho})^{\top}(2\Sigma_{\varrho}-\Sigma_{\rho})^{-1}(m_{\rho}-m_{\varrho})}}\right)-1.

We give references for these formulae in the
bibliography Section
11.4
. Note that neither the KL
divergence nor the
\chi^{2}
divergence are symmetric in their arguments, illustrating that the converse of Lemma
11.35
does not hold in general. Note further that the formula for the
\chi^{2}
divergence requires

2\Sigma_{\varrho}-\Sigma_{\rho}
to be positive definite. If this condition does not hold, then
\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{$\chi^{2}$}}}(\rho\|\varrho)=\infty
.
∎

Remark 11.41
.

The KL divergence can be minimized over dependence in its first argument without knowledge of the normalizing constant of the second; this fact is useful in variational inference (see Chapter
2
).
To show this we must extend the notion of
\mathsf{f}
-divergence in Definition
11.36
to allow the density in the second argument to be unnormalized.
The KL divergence is the only
\mathsf{f}
-divergence with the property that, for any
c>0
,

\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{f}}(q\|c\pi)-\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{f}}(q\|\pi)
is independent of
q.
(See Bibliography Section
11.4
for citation.)
For any probability density function
\pi
and statistical
\mathsf{f}
-divergence it holds that

\pi\in\argmin_{q\in\mathcal{P}}\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{f}}(q\|\pi)

and the minimizer is unique. Consequently, for any
c>0
,

\pi\in\argmin_{q\in\mathcal{P}}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|c\pi).

That is, the minimizer is independent of scaling factors on the target density, such as a normalization constant.
∎

The following result, which we use repeatedly in Chapter
5
, is known as the chain rule of KL divergence. Note the convention that the KL divergence between two conditionals
is defined by averaging over the marginal distribution of the variable used to condition.

Lemma 11.42
.

Let
\rho(u,v)=\rho(u)\rho(v|u)
and
\varrho(u,v)=\varrho(u)\varrho(v|u)
be two joint probability densities. It holds that

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\rho(u,v)\|\varrho(u,v)\bigr)=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\rho(u)\|\varrho(u)\bigr)+\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\rho(v|u)\|\varrho(v|u)\bigr),

where

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\rho(v|u)\|\varrho(v|u)\bigr):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\biggl(\frac{\rho(v|u)}{\varrho(v|u)}\biggr)\rho(v|u)\,dv\biggr]\rho(u)\,du.

Proof.

By direct calculation,

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\rho(u,v)\|\varrho(u,v)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\biggl(\frac{\rho(u,v)}{\varrho(u,v)}\biggr)\rho(u,v)\,dudv

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\biggl(\log\biggl(\frac{\rho(u)}{\varrho(u)}\biggr)+\log\biggl(\frac{\rho(v|u)}{\varrho(v|u)}\biggr)\biggr)\rho(u)\rho(v|u)\,dudv

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\biggl(\frac{\rho(u)}{\varrho(u)}\biggr)\rho(u)\,du+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\biggl(\frac{\rho(v|u)}{\varrho(v|u)}\biggr)\rho(v|u)\,dv\biggr]\rho(u)\,du

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\rho(u)\|\varrho(u)\bigr)+\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\rho(v|u)\|\varrho(v|u)\bigr),

as desired.
∎

11.2.2
Relationships Between
\mathsf{f}
-Divergences and Metrics

Here we establish bounds between some metrics and

\mathsf{f}
-divergences.
We continue to work under the simplifying assumption that

\rho,\varrho
are positive densities.

Lemma 11.43
.

The Hellinger and total variation distances
are upper bounded by the KL divergence as follows:

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\rho,\varrho)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\rho\|\varrho),\quad\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm TV}}}(\rho,\varrho)^{2}\leq\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\rho\|\varrho).

Proof.

It suffices to prove only the first inequality since it implies the second by Lemma
11.2
.
Define function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{+}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
by

\varphi(x):=x-1-\log{x}.

Then

\varphi^{\prime}(x)=1-\frac{1}{x},\quad\varphi^{\prime\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{x^{2}},\quad\lim_{x\to\infty}\varphi(x)=\lim_{x\downarrow 0}\varphi(x)=\infty,

and so the function is convex on its domain.
The minimum of
\varphi
is attained at
x=1
, and
\varphi(1)=0
;
hence
\varphi(x)\geq 0
for all
x\in(0,\infty).
It follows that, for all
x>0,

x-1\geq\log{x},\quad\sqrt{x}-1\geq\frac{1}{2}\log{x}.

Using this last inequality we can bound the Hellinger distance
as follows:

\begin{split}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm H}}}(\rho,\varrho)^{2}&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{\varrho(u)}{\rho(u)}}}}\right)^{2}\rho(u)\,du\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{\varrho(u)}{\rho(u)}-2\sqrt{\frac{\varrho(u)}{\rho(u)}}}}\right)\rho(u)\,du\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{\varrho(u)}{\rho(u)}}}}\right)\rho(u)\,du\leq-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\biggl({\frac{\varrho(u)}{\rho(u)}}\biggr)\rho(u)\,du=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\rho\|\varrho).\end{split}

∎

The following lemma shows that the
\chi^{2}
divergence upper bounds the KL divergence. By Lemma
11.43
this implies that
it also bounds the total variation
and Hellinger distances.

Lemma 11.44
.

The
\chi^{2}
divergence upper bounds
the KL divergence
as follows:

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\rho\|\varrho)\leq\log\Bigl(\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{$\chi^{2}$}}}(\rho\|\varrho)+1\Bigr),\quad\quad\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\rho\|\varrho)\leq\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{$\chi^{2}$}}}(\rho\|\varrho).

Proof.

The second inequality is a direct consequence of the first one, noting that, for
x\geq 0,

\log(x+1)\leq x.

To prove the first inequality note that by Jensen inequality

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\rho\|\varrho)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\biggl(\frac{\rho(u)}{\varrho(u)}\biggr)\rho(u)\,du

\displaystyle\leq\log\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\rho(u)}{\varrho(u)}\rho(u)\,du\biggr)

\displaystyle=\log\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\rho(u)}{\varrho(u)}\frac{\rho(u)}{\varrho(u)}\varrho(u)\,du\biggr)

\displaystyle=\log\Bigl(\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{$\chi^{2}$}}}(\rho\|\varrho)+1\Bigr),

where for the last equality we used that

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{$\chi^{2}$}}}(\rho\|\varrho)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\rho(u)}{\varrho(u)}-1}}\right)^{2}\,\varrho(u)\,du

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\rho(u)}{\varrho(u)}}}\right)^{2}\,\varrho(u)\,du-2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\rho(u)}{\varrho(u)}}}\right)\,\varrho(u)\,du+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho(u)\,du

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\rho(u)}{\varrho(u)}}}\right)^{2}\,\varrho(u)\,du-1.

∎

11.2.3
Invariance of
\mathsf{f}
-Divergences Under Invertible Transformations

This subsection discusses an important property satisfied by all
\mathsf{f}
-divergences: they are
invariant under invertible transformations of the underlying variables. Recall the notation for
pushforward from the preface. We start with the following lemma relating to
pushforwards. The proof is a straightforward consequence of change of variables.

Lemma 11.45
.

Let
\varrho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
let
g\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
be invertible everywhere on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Assume further that the determinant of the Jacobian of the inverse,

(11.19)

{\rm det}D(g^{-1})(u)=\Bigl({\rm det}Dg\bigl(g^{-1}(u)\bigr)\Bigr)^{-1},

is positive everywhere on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, for
u=g(z)
and function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(z)\varrho(z)\,dz=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigl(\varphi\circ g^{-1}\bigr)(u)\bigl(\varrho\circ g^{-1}\bigr)(u){\rm det}D(g^{-1})(u)\,du.

In particular, taking
\varphi(z)=\mathbb{1}_{g^{-1}(A)}(z)
, we obtain

\mathbb{P}\bigl(z\in g^{-1}(A)\bigr)=\mathbb{P}\bigl(u\in A\bigr)=\int_{A}\bigl(\varrho\circ g^{-1}\bigr)(u){\rm det}D(g^{-1})(u)\,du.

Thus

(11.20a)

\displaystyle g_{\sharp}\varrho(u)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\varrho\circ g^{-1}\bigr)(u){\rm det}D(g^{-1})(u),

(11.20b)

\displaystyle\log g_{\sharp}\varrho(u)

\displaystyle=\log\bigl(\varrho\circ g^{-1}\bigr)(u)+\log{\rm det}D\bigl(g^{-1}\bigr)(u).

The following theorem, concerning the invariance of the
\mathsf{f}
-divergence
\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{f}}
under invertible transformations, is a consequence of the preceding lemma.

Theorem 11.46
.

Let
T:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism such that
\det DT(u)>0

for every
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Then, for

\rho,\varrho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{f}}(\rho\|\varrho)=\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{f}}(T_{\sharp}\rho\|T_{\sharp}\varrho).

Proof.

Let
v=T(u)
denote the transformed variable. For an invertible transformation,
u=T^{-1}(v)
and
du=\det\bigl(DT^{-1}(v)\bigr)\,dv
. Performing a change of variables in the divergence we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{f}}(\rho\|\varrho)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{f}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\rho(u)}{\varrho(u)}}}\right)\varrho(u)\,du

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{f}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\rho\bigl(T^{-1}(v)\bigr)}{\varrho\bigl(T^{-1}(v)\bigr)}}}\right)\varrho\bigl(T^{-1}(v)\bigr)\det\bigl(DT^{-1}(v)\bigr)\,dv

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{f}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\rho\bigl(T^{-1}(v)\bigr)\det\bigl(DT^{-1}(v)\bigr)}{\varrho\bigl(T^{-1}(v)\bigr)\det\bigl(DT^{-1}(v)\bigr)}}}\right)\varrho\bigl(T^{-1}(v)\bigr)\det\bigl(DT^{-1}(v)\bigr)\,dv.

Using Lemma
11.45
we see that
\rho\bigl(T^{-1}(v)\bigr)\det\bigl(DT^{-1}(v)\bigr)=T_{\sharp}\rho(v)
denotes the density of the pushforward random variable
v
; thus we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{f}}(\rho\|\varrho)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{f}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{T_{\sharp}\rho(v)}{T_{\sharp}\varrho(v)}}}\right)T_{\sharp}\varrho(v)\,dv=\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{f}}(T_{\sharp}\rho\|T_{\sharp}\varrho).

∎

11.3
Scoring Rules

Probabilistic scoring rules quantify the accuracy of a given distribution, which we will refer to as the
forecast
,
with respect to a
true distribution
known only through
samples; the latter is sometimes termed
the verification.
Let
\rho
and
\varrho
denote the densities of the forecast and verification distributions, respectively.

Definition 11.47
.

A
scoring rule
is a function
\mathsf{S}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that assigns a
score

\mathsf{S}(\rho,v)
to a sample
v\sim\varrho
with respect
to
\rho
.
∎

We follow the convention here that scoring rules are negatively oriented, meaning that a lower score indicates a better forecast. To compare the forecast and verification distributions, we define:

Definition 11.48
.

Given a scoring rule, the
expected score

\overline{\mathsf{S}}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

is defined by

\overline{\mathsf{S}}(\rho,\varrho)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{v\sim\varrho}[\mathsf{S}(\rho,v)]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{S}(\rho,v)\varrho(v)\,dv.

∎

It is natural to ask whether an expected score can be used to define a divergence between
\rho,\varrho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).
With this goal in mind we make
the following definition:

Definition 11.49
.

A scoring rule
\mathsf{S}
is called
proper
if, for all
\rho,\varrho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\overline{\mathsf{S}}(\varrho,\varrho)\leq\overline{\mathsf{S}}(\rho,\varrho)
. A scoring rule is called
strictly proper
when equality holds if and only if
\rho=\varrho
.
∎

Thus, for a proper scoring rule, forecasting with the true distribution results in the lowest expected score. For a strictly proper scoring rule, the lowest expected score can only be attained when forecasting with the true distribution. Using this property, scoring rules can
be used to define a divergence if strict propriety is imposed:

Theorem 11.50
.

Let
\mathsf{S}
be a strictly proper scoring rule and define

(11.21)

\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{S}}(\rho\|\varrho)\coloneqq\overline{\mathsf{S}}(\rho,\varrho)-\overline{\mathsf{S}}(\varrho,\varrho).

Then
\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{S}}
defines a divergence. Conversely, if
\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{S}}
is a divergence, then
\mathsf{S}
is strictly proper.

Proof.

For a proper scoring rule, the distance is non-negative:
\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{S}}(\rho,\varrho)\geq 0
. If
\mathsf{S}
is strictly proper then
\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{S}}(\rho,\varrho)=0
if and only if
\rho=\varrho
. The converse is immediate.
∎

In the following subsections we present five (probabilistic) scoring
rules: the energy score, the continuous ranked probability score, the quantile score, the logarithmic score, and the Dawid–Sebastiani score. We then discuss the class of kernel scores, the topic of noise in the verification
and we introduce deterministic scoring rules.

11.3.1
Energy Score

We first consider the energy score, which is closely related to the energy distance studied in Subsection
11.1.5
.

Definition 11.51
.

For
\beta\in(0,2]
,
the
energy score

\mathsf{ES}_{\beta}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined by

(11.22)

\mathsf{ES}_{\beta}(\rho,v)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}|u-v|^{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}^{(u,u^{\prime})\sim\rho\otimes\rho}|u-u^{\prime}|^{\beta}.

The
expected energy score

\overline{\mathsf{ES}}_{\beta}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is then defined by

(11.23)

\overline{\mathsf{ES}}_{\beta}(\rho,\varrho)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{v\sim\varrho}[\mathsf{ES}_{\beta}(\rho,v)]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{ES}_{\beta}(\rho,v)\varrho(v)\,dv.

∎

Lemma 11.52
.

The expected energy score (
11.23
) can be written, for
\beta\in(0,2)
, as

(11.24)

\overline{\mathsf{ES}}_{\beta}(\rho,\varrho)=\frac{\beta 2^{\beta-2}\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\beta}{2})}{\pi^{d/2}\Gamma(1-\frac{\beta}{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|\varphi_{\rho}(u)-\varphi_{\varrho}(u)|^{2}}{|u|^{d+\beta}}\,du+\overline{\mathsf{ES}}_{\beta}(\varrho,\varrho),

where
\varphi_{\rho}
and
\varphi_{\varrho}
are the characteristic
functions of
\rho
and
\varrho
, respectively. Hence, for
\beta\in(0,2)
,
the energy score is strictly proper.

Proof.

We give a reference for (
11.24
) in the
bibliography; its proof is omitted here for reasons of brevity. The strict propriety follows because
two characteristic functions
\varphi_{\rho}(u),\varphi_{\varrho}(u)
are identical if and only if

\rho=\varrho.

∎

Recall the squared energy distance in Definition
11.27
, given by

(11.25)

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm E}}}^{2}(\rho,\varrho):=2\mathbb{E}^{(u,v)\sim\rho\otimes\varrho}|u-v|-\mathbb{E}^{(u,u^{\prime})\sim\rho\otimes\rho}|u-u^{\prime}|-\mathbb{E}^{(v,v^{\prime})\sim\varrho\otimes\varrho}|v-v^{\prime}|.

The following lemma makes an explicit connection between the energy score, with
\beta=1
, and the energy distance.

Lemma 11.53
.

The energy score
\mathsf{ES}_{1}(\rho,v)
is related to the energy distance by the identity

\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm E}}}^{2}(\rho,\varrho)=\overline{\mathsf{ES}}_{1}(\rho,\varrho)-\overline{\mathsf{ES}}_{1}(\varrho,\varrho).

Proof.

First note that

\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{ES}}_{\beta}(\rho,\varrho)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{(u,v)\sim\rho\otimes\varrho}|u-v|^{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}^{(u,u^{\prime})\sim\rho\otimes\rho}|u-u^{\prime}|^{\beta},

\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{ES}}_{\beta}(\varrho,\varrho)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}^{(u,u^{\prime})\sim\varrho\otimes\varrho}|u-u^{\prime}|^{\beta}.

Then, for
\beta=1
,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{ES}}_{1}(\rho,\varrho)-\overline{\mathsf{ES}}_{1}(\varrho,\varrho)=\mathbb{E}^{(u,v)\sim\rho\otimes\varrho}|u-v|-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}^{(u,u^{\prime})\sim\rho\otimes\rho}|u-u^{\prime}|-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}^{(u,u^{\prime})\sim\varrho\otimes\varrho}|u-u^{\prime}|,

which is indeed half of the squared energy distance.
∎

We are now in a position to prove Lemma
11.28
:

Proof of Lemma
11.28
.

From Lemma
11.53
it follows that
\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm E}}}^{2}(\rho,\varrho)
is a divergence. As
a consequence
\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm E}}}(\rho,\varrho)
is also a divergence. It remains to show that

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm E}}}(\rho,\varrho)
is symmetric and satisfies the triangle inequality.
The proof follows from noting that, from Lemma
11.53
and equation (
11.24
)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm E}}}^{2}(\rho,\varrho)

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathsf{ES}}_{1}(\rho,\varrho)-\overline{\mathsf{ES}}_{1}(\varrho,\varrho)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2})}{2\pi^{d/2}\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|\varphi_{\rho}(u)-\varphi_{\varrho}(u)|^{2}}{|u|^{d+1}}\,du.

Using the fact that
\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm E}}}(\rho,\varrho)
is defined as a weighted
L^{2}
-norm
of the difference between the characteristic functions of the pair
(\rho,\varrho)

leads to the desired metric structure. In particular the symmetry and triangle inequality
follow from the properties of norms.
∎

Lemma
11.52
holds for
\beta\in(0,2).
For
\beta=2
strict propriety
is lost.

Lemma 11.54
.

The energy score
\mathsf{ES}_{2}(\rho,v)
is proper, but not strictly proper.

Proof.

Notice that

\displaystyle\mathsf{ES}_{2}(\rho,v)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}|u|^{2}-2\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}[u]^{\top}v+|v|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}(\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}|u|^{2}-2\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}[u]^{\top}\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}[u]+\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}|u|^{2})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}[u]^{\top}\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}[u]-2\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}[u]^{\top}v+|v|^{2}

\displaystyle=\bigl|\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}[u]-v\bigr|^{2}.

The minimizer of
\mathsf{J}(b):=\mathbb{E}^{v\sim\varrho}|b-v|^{2}
is
b^{\star}=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\varrho}[u],
which shows that
\mathsf{ES}_{2}
is proper. It is not strictly proper, since for any other distribution
\widetilde{\varrho}
with
\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\varrho}[u]=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\widetilde{\varrho}}[u]
it holds that
\overline{\mathsf{ES}}_{2}(\varrho,\varrho)=\overline{\mathsf{ES}}_{2}(\widetilde{\varrho},\varrho).

∎

Remark 11.55
.

When
\beta=2
the expected energy score
\mathbb{E}^{v\sim\varrho}[\mathsf{ES}_{2}(\rho,v)]
is the
mean-square error
between
\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}[u]
and
v
. Its square root, the
root-mean-square error (RMSE)
is often used for evaluating probabilistic forecasts, despite lacking strict propriety. Furthermore, in various places in Chapters
9

and
10
, we use variants on the mean-square error to define loss functions from which to learn data assimilation algorithms.
∎

11.3.2
Continuous Ranked Probability Score

This score is only defined for probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
and real-valued random variables. Similar to the Wasserstein-
1
distance
in the one-dimensional setting studied in Subsection
11.1.3
, the continuous ranked probability score (CRPS) is based on comparing cumulative distribution functions. Recall the notation

F_{\rho}
for the cumulative distribution function associated to probability density function
\rho.

To introduce the definition, we let

\mathbb{1}_{u\geq v}
denote the indicator function of the set
\{u\in\mathbb{R}:u\geq v\}
; this is also known as a Heaviside step function.
We will also employ natural generalizations of this notation in which
\geq
is replaced by

>
,
\leq
and
<
.

Definition 11.56
.

The
continuous ranked probability score

\mathsf{CRPS}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}

is defined by

(11.26)

\mathsf{CRPS}(\rho,v)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(F_{\rho}(u)-\mathbb{1}_{u\geq v}(u)\bigr)^{2}\,du.

The
expected continuous ranked probability score

\overline{\mathsf{CRPS}}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is then defined by

\overline{\mathsf{CRPS}}(\rho,\varrho)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{v\sim\varrho}[\mathsf{CRPS}(\rho,v)]=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathsf{CRPS}(\rho,v)\varrho(v)\,dv.

∎

Remark 11.57
.

The indicator function
\mathbb{1}_{u\geq v}
is the cumulative distribution function of a Dirac mass at
v
.
Thus, the CRPS compares two cumulative distribution functions, one for the
forecast and one for the verification;
in particular it is the square of the
L^{2}
distance between the two cumulative distribution functions.
∎

The following lemma shows that, in dimension
d=1
, the CRPS
is equivalent to the energy score in Definition
11.51
with
\beta=1
. Thus, the energy
score with
\beta=1
provides a natural generalization of CRPS to more than one dimension.

Lemma 11.58
.

For
\rho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})
and
v\in\mathbb{R},
it holds that

(11.27)

\mathsf{CRPS}(\rho,v)=\mathsf{ES}_{1}(\rho,v)=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}|u-v|-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}^{(u,u^{\prime})\sim\rho\otimes\rho}|u-u^{\prime}|.

Proof.

First, let us recall that the absolute value of a difference can be written as

|u-v|=\int_{v}^{\infty}\mathbb{1}_{z\leq u}(z)\,dz+\int_{-\infty}^{v}\mathbb{1}_{z>u}(z)\,dz,

by considering the two cases
v\leq u
and
v>u
.
Taking an expectation with respect to
u\sim\rho
and applying Fubini’s theorem we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}|u-v|

\displaystyle=\int_{v}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{1}_{z\leq u}(z)\rho(u)\,dudz+\int_{-\infty}^{v}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{1}_{z>u}(z)\rho(u)\,dudz

\displaystyle=\int_{v}^{\infty}\bigl(1-F_{\rho}(z)\bigr)\,dz+\int_{-\infty}^{v}F_{\rho}(z)\,dz

(11.28)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(1-F_{\rho}(z)\bigr)\mathbb{1}_{z\geq v}(z)\,dz+\int_{\mathbb{R}}F_{\rho}(z)\mathbb{1}_{z<v}(z)\,dz.

Following similar steps for the second term in (
11.27
) and taking an expectation over
v\sim\rho
, we have

(11.29)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}^{(u,v)\sim\rho\otimes\rho}\,|u-v|=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}^{v\sim\rho}\Bigl(\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}|u-v|\Bigr)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}F_{\rho}(z)\bigl(1-F_{\rho}(z)\bigr)\,dz.

Lastly, using
\mathbb{1}_{z<v}(z)=1-\mathbb{1}_{z\geq v}(z)
, and subtracting (
11.29
)
from (
11.28
), gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\rho}|u-v|-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}^{(u,u^{\prime})\sim\rho\otimes\rho}\,|u-u^{\prime}|

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\mathbb{1}_{z\geq v}(z)-2F_{\rho}(z)\mathbb{1}_{z\geq v}(z)+F_{\rho}(z)^{2}\Bigr)\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\mathbb{1}_{z\geq v}(z)^{2}-2F_{\rho}(z)\mathbb{1}_{z\geq v}(z)+F_{\rho}(z)^{2}\Bigr)\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(F_{\rho}(z)-\mathbb{1}_{z\geq v}(z)\bigr)^{2}\,dz=\mathsf{CRPS}(\rho,v).

∎

The characterization of the CRPS in (
11.27
) shows that it is composed of two parts:
a calibration term that quantifies closeness of the forecast to
v
and a sharpness term that is related to the spread of the distribution.

Example 11.59
.

Consider a forecast distribution which is deterministic:
\rho=\delta_{w}
and

F_{\rho}(u)=\mathbb{1}_{u\geq w}(u)
. Then either (
11.26
) or (
11.27
)
show that the CRPS reduces to the absolute error between the point
w
and sample
v
.
∎

Lemma 11.60
.

The expected CRPS for forecast and verification distributions with probability density functions
\rho
and
\varrho
, respectively, is given by

(11.30)

\overline{\mathsf{CRPS}}(\rho,\varrho)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(F_{\rho}(u)-F_{\varrho}(u)\bigr)^{2}\,du+\int_{\mathbb{R}}F_{\varrho}(u)\bigl(1-F_{\varrho}(u)\bigr)\,du.

Proof.

Start with form (
11.26
) of the CRPS. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{v\sim\varrho}\bigl[\mathsf{CRPS}(\rho,v)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E}^{v\sim\varrho}\Bigl[\bigl(F_{\rho}(u)-\mathbb{1}_{u\geq v}(u)\bigr)^{2}\Bigr]\,du

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E}^{v\sim\varrho}\Bigl[F_{\rho}(u)^{2}-2F_{\rho}(u)\mathbb{1}_{u\geq v}(u)+\mathbb{1}_{u\geq v}(u)\Bigr]\,du,

noting that
\mathbb{1}_{u\geq v}(u)^{2}=\mathbb{1}_{u\geq v}(u)
. Since

\mathbb{E}^{v\sim\varrho}[\mathbb{1}_{u\geq v}(u)]=F_{\varrho}(u)
, we obtain the result after rearranging.
∎

Remark 11.61
.

A direct consequence of Lemma
11.60
is that the divergence (
11.21
) defined by the CRPS takes the form

\displaystyle\mathsf{D_{CRPS}}(\rho\|\varrho)=\overline{\mathsf{CRPS}}(\rho,\varrho)-\overline{\mathsf{CRPS}}(\varrho,\varrho)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(F_{\rho}(u)-F_{\varrho}(u)\bigr)^{2}\,du,

which is known as the squared Cramér’s distance. Thus, we have shown that in one dimension CRPS agrees with the energy score
\mathsf{ES}_{1}
(Lemma
11.58
), the divergence induced by CRPS agrees with the squared Cramér’s distance (Lemma
11.60
), and the squared energy distance is exactly twice the squared Cramér’s distance (Lemma
11.53
).
∎

Example 11.62
.

For a univariate Gaussian density
\rho=\mathcal{N}(m,\sigma^{2})
, the
CRPS with respect to a single observation
v
has the form

\mathsf{CRPS}(\rho,v)=\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\varphi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{v-m}{\sigma}}}\right)+\frac{v-m}{\sigma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{v-m}{\sigma}}}\right)-1}}\right)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}}}\right),

where
\varphi
and
\Phi
denote the probability density function and cumulative distribution function of a standard Gaussian distribution, respectively. We give a reference for this formula in the
bibliography Section
11.4
. The formula illustrates that, for a single observation
v,
the CRPS
favors forecasts whose mean is close to
v
and whose variance is
small. Indeed, if degenerate Gaussians are allowed, the minimum value
zero is attained when
m=v
and
\sigma=0,
corresponding to the
Dirac measure at
v.

∎

11.3.3
Quantile Score

Like the CRPS and
Wasserstein-
1
distance in the one-dimensional setting, the
quantile score is based on comparing cumulative distribution functions; and like CRPS it is only defined for probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
and for real-valued random variables. The quantile score introduces additional flexibility, in comparison to the CRPS, through a parameter
\alpha\in(0,1)
that allows one to penalize deviations at a specific quantile level. To introduce the quantile score we first define the
quantile function and then demonstrate how that
function can be derived through a minimization problem. Finally, we link the
quantile score to the CRPS.

Definition 11.63
.

Given a probability density function
\varrho
corresponding
to a random variable on
\mathbb{R}
with cumulative distribution function
