F_{\varrho}
, the corresponding
quantile function

q_{\varrho}:(0,1)\to\mathbb{R}

is the inverse of the cumulative distribution function:

2
2

2

For ease of presentation, we assume throughout invertibility of the cumulative distribution function; the ideas generalize to the non-invertible case by considering the generalized inverse distribution function.

q_{\varrho}(\alpha):=F_{\varrho}^{-1}(\alpha)
.
∎

Example 11.64
.

Recall that the cumulative distribution function of the Dirac mass at
v
is the Heaviside
step function
\mathbb{1}_{u\geq v}(u).

Informally, by turning this function on its side, we see that its inverse is the constant function taking value
v
in
(0,1)
. Thus this constant function
v
is the quantile function associated with the Dirac mass at
v
. To see this more formally, note that for any
\alpha\in(0,1),
we have that
F^{-1}_{\delta_{v}}(\alpha)=\inf\{u:F_{\delta_{v}}(u)\geq\alpha\}=v,
where here we have used the generalized inverse distribution function.
∎

Note that we have already used the quantile function, in Lemma

11.12
, to define the Wasserstein distance in one
dimension. The quantile function is the solution to the
\alpha
-parameterized family of equations

F_{\varrho}\bigl(q_{\varrho}(\alpha)\bigr)=\alpha.

We now prove a lemma which characterizes the quantile function in a different way:
showing that the quantile function (of
\alpha
) is also the minimizer of a scalar-valued function
L_{\alpha,\varrho}(\cdot)
;
the minimizer depends parametrically on both
\alpha
and
\varrho
and, for fixed
\varrho
delivers the
quantile function (of
\alpha
). To this end let
\alpha\in(0,1).
Start by defining the
\alpha
-parameterized family of functions
h_{\alpha}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
by

(11.31)

h_{\alpha}(u)=\begin{cases}(1-\alpha)|u|,&\quad u<0,\\
\alpha|u|,&\quad u\geq 0.\end{cases}

Function
h_{\alpha}
is the
quantile loss
and may also be written

h_{\alpha}(u)=-(\mathbb{1}_{u\leq 0}-\alpha)u
or as

h_{\alpha}(u)=\begin{cases}-(1-\alpha)u,&\quad u<0,\\
\alpha u,&\quad u\geq 0.\end{cases}

Using this function, together with a probability density function
\varrho
, we define another
\alpha
-parameterized family of functions
L_{\alpha,\varrho}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
by

(11.32)

L_{\alpha,\varrho}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}^{v\sim\varrho}\bigl[h_{\alpha}(v-\theta)\bigr].

The following lemma uses
L_{\alpha,\varrho}(\cdot)
to provide an alternative characterization
of the quantile function:

Lemma 11.65
.

For
\alpha\in(0,1),
it holds that

q_{\varrho}(\alpha)\in\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}}\,L_{\alpha,\varrho}(\theta).

Proof.

Note that, using (
11.31
),

L_{\alpha,\varrho}(\theta)=(\alpha-1)\int_{-\infty}^{\theta}(v-\theta)\varrho(v)\,dv+\alpha\int_{\theta}^{\infty}(v-\theta)\varrho(v)\,dv.

Differentiating with respect to
\theta
yields

\displaystyle D_{\theta}L_{\alpha,\varrho}(\theta)

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha)\int_{-\infty}^{\theta}\varrho(v)\,dv-\alpha\int_{\theta}^{\infty}\varrho(v)\,dv

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\theta}\varrho(v)\,dv-\alpha

\displaystyle=F_{\varrho}(\theta)-\alpha.

Setting the derivative to zero yields the desired result.
∎

We may now define, and analyze, the quantile score.

Definition 11.66
.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
the
quantile score
at level
\alpha
,

{\mathsf{QS}}_{\alpha}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
is defined by

\mathsf{QS}_{\alpha}(\rho,v)=h_{\alpha}\bigl(v-q_{\rho}(\alpha)\bigr).

The
expected quantile score

\overline{\mathsf{QS}}_{\alpha}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is then defined by

\overline{\mathsf{QS}}_{\alpha}(\rho,\varrho)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{v\sim\varrho}[\mathsf{QS}_{\alpha}(\rho,v)]=\mathbb{E}^{v\sim\varrho}[h_{\alpha}\bigl(v-q_{\rho}(\alpha)\bigr)].

∎

Remark 11.67
.

In view of Example
11.64
we see that the quantile score
is found by applying the hinge function
h_{\alpha}
to the difference between the quantile functions
for a Dirac mass at
v
and for
\rho,
evaluated at
\alpha.
We note also the following two
alternative expressions for the quantile score at level
\alpha:

(11.33)

\mathsf{QS}_{\alpha}(\rho,v)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{1}_{v\leq q_{\rho}(\alpha)}-\alpha}}\right)(q_{\rho}(\alpha)-v),\quad\mathsf{QS}_{\alpha}(\rho,v)=h_{\alpha}\bigl(v-F_{\rho}^{-1}(\alpha)\bigr).

∎

The following is a direct consequence of Lemma
11.65
:

Proposition 11.68
.

For every
\alpha\in(0,1)
, the quantile score
\mathsf{QS}_{\alpha}
is a proper, but not strictly proper, scoring rule.

Proof.

Let

\rho^{\star}\in\argmin_{\rho}\,\overline{\mathsf{QS}}_{\alpha}(\rho,\varrho).

We show that, for all
\alpha\in(0,1),

\rho^{\star}=\varrho

is a solution of this minimization problem. It follows that
\mathsf{QS}_{\alpha}
is
a proper scoring rule.
To see this note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{v\sim\varrho}[\mathsf{QS}_{\alpha}(\rho,v)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{v\sim\varrho}\Bigl[h_{\alpha}\bigl(v-q_{\rho}(\alpha)\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=L_{\alpha,\varrho}\bigl(q_{\rho}(\alpha)\bigr).

By Lemma
11.65
the optimal
\rho
will be one for
which
q_{\rho}(\alpha)=q_{\varrho}(\alpha).

This can be achieved by setting
\rho=\varrho.
Finally, note that
\mathsf{QS}_{\alpha}
is not strictly proper since a single quantile does not identify the full forecast distribution.
∎

Our final result on quantile scores shows that the CRPS can also be computed as twice the integral of the quantile
score over all quantiles.

Lemma 11.69
.

Assume that
\rho
has finite first moment. Then

\mathsf{CRPS}(\rho,v)=2\int_{0}^{1}\mathsf{QS}_{\alpha}(\rho,v)\,d\alpha,

and hence

\overline{\mathsf{CRPS}}(\rho,\varrho)=2\int_{0}^{1}\overline{\mathsf{QS}}_{\alpha}(\rho,\varrho)\,d\alpha.

Proof.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
, recall that
q_{\rho}(\alpha)=F_{\rho}^{-1}(\alpha)
so that the first identity in (
11.33
) gives

\mathsf{QS}_{\alpha}(\rho,v)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{1}_{v\leq F_{\rho}^{-1}(\alpha)}-\alpha}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{\rho}^{-1}(\alpha)-v}}\right).

We proceed by applying the change of variables
u=F_{\rho}^{-1}(\alpha)
to the integrated quantile score. Note that this change of variables implies that
d\alpha=\rho(u)\,du
. Thus

\displaystyle 2\int_{0}^{1}\mathsf{QS}_{\alpha}(\rho,v)\,d\alpha

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{1}_{v\leq F_{\rho}^{-1}(\alpha)}-\alpha}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{\rho}^{-1}(\alpha)-v}}\right)\,d\alpha

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{1}_{v\leq u}-F_{\rho}(u)}}\right)(u-v)\rho(u)\,du.

We recognize that, for
u\neq v
,
D_{u}\bigl(\mathbb{1}_{v\leq u}-F_{\rho}(u)\bigr)^{2}=-2\bigl(\mathbb{1}_{v\leq u}-F_{\rho}(u)\bigr)\rho(u)
. Applying integration by parts gives us

\displaystyle 2\int_{0}^{1}\mathsf{QS}_{\alpha}(\rho,v)\,d\alpha

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}D_{u}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{1}_{v\leq u}-F_{\rho}(u)}}\right)^{2}(u-v)\,du

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathbb{1}_{v\leq u}-F_{\rho}(u)\bigr)^{2}(u-v)\Bigl|_{u=-\infty}^{u=\infty}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(\mathbb{1}_{v\leq u}-F_{\rho}(u)\bigr)^{2}\,du.

We assume that the boundary terms in the integration by parts vanish, and establish this fact below.
Under this assumption we have shown that

\displaystyle 2\int_{0}^{1}\mathsf{QS}_{\alpha}(\rho,v)\,d\alpha

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(\mathbb{1}_{v\leq u}-F_{\rho}(u)\bigr)^{2}\,du=\mathsf{CRPS}(\rho,v),

as desired.

Now consider the boundary terms. By Markov’s inequality we have that, for
u
large and positive,

1-F_{\rho}(u)=\mathbb{P}(w\geq u)\leq\mathbb{P}(|w|\geq|u|)\leq\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{w\sim\rho}|w|}{|u|}.

Similarly, for
u
large and negative,

F_{\rho}(u)=\mathbb{P}(w\leq u)\leq\mathbb{P}(|w|\geq|u|)\leq\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{w\sim\rho}|w|}{|u|}.

Hence
\bigl(1-F_{\rho}(u)\bigr)^{2}(u-v)\to 0
as
u\to\infty.
The limit as
u\to-\infty
is similar.
∎

11.3.4
Logarithmic Score

The logarithmic score is based on the intuitive idea that
\rho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
provides an accurate forecast for verification sample
v\sim\varrho
if
\rho(v)
is large. Noting that the logarithm function is monotonically increasing and that scoring rules are negatively oriented by convention,
the logarithmic score evaluates the negative logarithm of the forecast probability density function
\rho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
at a sample
v\sim\varrho.
In the following, we tacitly assume we work with positive densities.

Definition 11.70
.

The
logarithmic score

\mathsf{LS}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

(11.34)

\mathsf{LS}(\rho,v)\coloneqq-\log\rho(v).

The
expected logarithmic score

\overline{\mathsf{LS}}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is then defined by

\overline{\mathsf{LS}}(\rho,\varrho)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{v\sim\varrho}[\mathsf{LS}(\rho,v)]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{LS}(\rho,v)\varrho(v)\,dv.

∎

The logarithmic score is also sometimes referred to as the
ignorance score
when the logarithm is in base 2. Notice that the logarithmic score penalizes heavily forecasts
\rho
that place low probability in outcomes that materialize: for small
\rho(v),

\mathsf{LS}(\rho,v)\coloneqq-\log\rho(v)
is very large. Recall the KL divergence given in Definition
11.39
.

Lemma 11.71
.

The logarithmic score is a strictly proper scoring rule.
The divergence resulting from the expected logarithmic score is the KL divergence:

(11.35)

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\varrho\|\rho)=\overline{\mathsf{LS}}(\rho,\varrho)-\overline{\mathsf{LS}}(\varrho,\varrho).

Proof.

We have that

\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{LS}}(\rho,\varrho)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{v\sim\varrho}[\mathsf{LS}(\rho,v)]=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\rho(v)\varrho(v)\,dv,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{LS}}(\varrho,\varrho)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{v\sim\varrho}[\mathsf{LS}(\varrho,v)]=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\varrho(v)\varrho(v)\,dv.

Recalling that

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\varrho\|\rho)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\biggl(\frac{\varrho(v)}{\rho(v)}\biggr)\varrho(v)\,dv

delivers the desired result.
∎

11.3.5
Dawid–Sebastiani Score

The Dawid–Sebastiani score controls the first and second moments of the forecast.
In the following definition, we tacitly assume the covariance
C
to be positive definite.

Definition 11.72
.

The
Dawid–Sebastiani score

\mathsf{DS}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

(11.36)

\mathsf{DS}(\rho,v):=|v-m|_{C}^{2}+\log\bigl(\det(C)\bigr),

where
m
and
C
are the mean and covariance of
\rho
. The

expected Dawid–Sebastiani score

\overline{\mathsf{DS}}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
is then defined by

(11.37)

\overline{\mathsf{DS}}(\rho,\varrho):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{DS}(\rho,v)\varrho(v)\,dv.

∎

Remark 11.73
.

The Dawid–Sebastiani score is equivalent, up to a linear transformation, to the logarithmic score in the Gaussian case. It can be used, however, for general distributions with finite mean and covariance.
∎

The Dawid–Sebastiani score is proper, but not strictly proper.
This is similar to the
\mathsf{ES}_{2}
score, which reduces to controlling the first moment and is not strictly proper – see Lemma
11.54
; the Dawid–Sebastiani score controls first and second moments and hence it is not strictly proper.

11.3.6
Kernel Scores

Recall Definition
11.19
of a kernel.
A kernel score is a scoring rule associated to a kernel.

Definition 11.74
.

For kernel
c:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, the
kernel score

\mathsf{KS}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

(11.38)

\mathsf{KS}(\rho,v)=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{u\sim\rho}[c(u,v)]-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(u,u^{\prime})\sim\rho\otimes\rho}[c(u,u^{\prime})].

The
expected kernel score

\overline{\mathsf{KS}}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
is then defined by

(11.39)

\overline{\mathsf{KS}}(\rho,\varrho)=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(u,v)\sim\rho\otimes\varrho}[c(u,v)]-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(u,u^{\prime})\sim\rho\otimes\rho}[c(u,u^{\prime})].

∎

Example 11.75
.

The kernel score with kernel
c(u,v)=|u-v|^{\beta}
, for
\beta\in(0,2)
, is equivalent to the energy score
\mathsf{ES}_{\beta}
(Definition
11.51
).
It follows, by Lemma
11.58
, that the continuous ranked probability score (CRPS; Definition
11.56
) is also a kernel score of this type, with
\beta=1
and
d=1
.
∎

Kernel scores have different propriety properties depending on the choice of kernel.
In this context it is natural to define

(11.40)

\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{KS}}(\rho,\varrho):=\overline{\mathsf{KS}}(\rho,\varrho)-\overline{\mathsf{KS}}(\varrho,\varrho)

and to ask when this is indeed a divergence. As a first step we state
the following theorem, and cite a reference to its proof in the bibliography.

Theorem 11.76
.

The kernel score with a negative definite kernel is proper.

The following lemma establishes the relationship between kernel scores and the maximum mean discrepancy (MMD; Definition
11.20
), and gives a condition under which a kernel score
is
strictly proper
.

Lemma 11.77
.

Let
c:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a positive definite kernel, and let

\mathsf{KS}
denote the kernel score associated with the kernel
-c.
Then

(11.41)

\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MMD}}}^{2}(\rho,\varrho)=\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{KS}}(\rho,\varrho).

The kernel score
\mathsf{KS}
is proper. Furthermore, if
c
is
characteristic, then
\mathsf{KS}
is strictly proper.

Proof.

Computing the right-hand side of (
11.41
) with kernel
-c
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{KS}}(\rho,\varrho)=-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(u,v)\sim\rho\otimes\varrho}[c(u,v)]+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(u,u^{\prime})\sim\rho\otimes\rho}[c(u,u^{\prime})]+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(v,v^{\prime})\sim\varrho\otimes\varrho}[c(v,v^{\prime})];

this is exactly
\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MMD}}}^{2}(\rho,\varrho)
with kernel
c
.

From the definition of positive and negative definiteness for kernels (Definition
11.19
), we can see that if
c
is positive definite,
-c
is negative definite (the converse is not necessarily true); thus, by Theorem
11.76
,
\mathsf{KS}
with kernel
-c
is proper.
If
c
is characteristic, then
\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MMD}}}
is a metric (Remark
11.25
), and hence a divergence (Lemma
11.35
). Using Theorem
11.50
, this implies that
\mathsf{KS}
is strictly proper.
∎

As with the energy score and CRPS (both types of kernel scores, see Example
11.75
), a practical advantage of kernel scores is that they can be readily empiricalized.
Specifically, given independent samples

\{u^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N}\sim\rho,
the kernel score may be estimated by

\mathsf{KS}(\rho,v)\approx\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}c(u^{(i)},v)-\frac{1}{2N(N-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}c(u^{(i)},u^{(j)}),

where we have dropped the diagonal terms in the second term and renormalized, similarly to the discussion
in Remark
11.29
.

Remark 11.78
.

In inverse problems and data assimilation, it is often useful to consider probabilistic objective functions
that are amenable to empirical approximation; thus they can be used given finite data samples as in the data assumptions arising throughout this book. Many metrics and divergences will not be amenable to quantifying distance between two probability measures when both are given only through samples, and are hence a sum of
Dirac (empirical) measures.
Examples include the total variation and Hellinger distances from Definition
11.1
and the KL divergence from Definition
11.39
. On the other hand, the transportation metrics from Subsection
11.1.3
naturally quantify the closeness between Dirac masses, and, more generally, between empirical measures; see Example
11.11
.
Metrics and divergences defined via a scoring rule are in principle naturally
approximated by empirical measures in at least one of their arguments, since in the construction of
expected
score
, which leads to a divergence, empiricalization can be used to replace the expectation.
The energy score itself is
also
defined via an expectation and as a consequence the resulting metric, the energy distance, can be evaluated with both inputs given as samples; the same is true of MMD. However, not all divergences and metrics defined by scoring rules can be evaluated with both inputs given as samples; for example the logarithmic
score, which leads to the KL divergence, cannot. This is because the underlying score requires pointwise evaluation of the underlying density, rather than only an expectation with respect to it.
For this reason KL divergence
needs to be used carefully when designing loss functions that are to be empiricalized in practice
as the resulting objective functions are often only
empiricalizable with respect to one of their two inputs.
∎

11.3.7
Noise in the Verification

In the presence of noise in the verification,
the scoring rule will generally favor more dispersed forecasts than if the noise were not present. To account for this we consider scores calculated using noisy data
\widetilde{v}
drawn from

\widetilde{v}|v\sim r(\cdot\,|v)
, given the noise-free verification
v\sim\varrho
. We can then modify the Definition
11.49
of propriety as follows:

Definition 11.79
.

Given noise distribution
r(\cdot\,|\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
and probability distribution
\varrho
define

\widetilde{\varrho}(\widetilde{v})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}r(\widetilde{v}|v)\varrho(v)\,dv.

A scoring rule
\mathsf{S}
is called
proper with respect to the noise distribution
r
if, for all
\rho
,
\mathbb{E}^{\widetilde{v}\sim\widetilde{\varrho}}\bigl[{\mathsf{S}}(\varrho,\widetilde{v})\bigr]\leq\mathbb{E}^{\widetilde{v}\sim\widetilde{\varrho}}\bigl[\mathsf{S}(\rho,\widetilde{v})\bigr]
. It is called
strictly proper with respect to
r
when equality holds if and only if
\rho=\varrho
.
∎

Proposition 11.80
.

Given noise distribution
r(\cdot\,|\cdot)
and probability distribution
\varrho
define

\widetilde{\varrho}(\widetilde{v})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}r(\widetilde{v}|v)\varrho(v)\,dv.

If
\mathsf{S}
is proper, then
\widetilde{\mathsf{S}}(\rho,\cdot):=\mathsf{S}(\widetilde{\rho},\cdot)

is proper with respect to
r
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\widetilde{v}\sim\widetilde{\varrho}}\bigl[\widetilde{\mathsf{S}}(\rho,\widetilde{v})\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\widetilde{v}\sim\widetilde{\varrho}}\bigl[\mathsf{S}(\widetilde{\rho},\widetilde{v})\bigr]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}^{\widetilde{v}\sim\widetilde{\varrho}}\bigl[\mathsf{S}(\widetilde{\varrho},\widetilde{v})\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\widetilde{v}\sim\widetilde{\varrho}}\bigl[\widetilde{\mathsf{S}}(\varrho,\widetilde{v})\bigr],

where the second line follows from the propriety of
\mathsf{S}
.
∎

Note that propriety does not guarantee an accurate ranking of forecast distributions if the forecast distributions are evaluated on verifications with different levels of noise. To ensure this property, the scoring rule must be modified for each level of noise such that the expected score is the same as it would be if there were no noise. A scoring rule possessing this property is
known as
unbiased
.

11.3.8
Distance-Like Deterministic Scoring Rule

The scoring rules introduced so far are probabilistic
in that they compare a probability distribution with a point in the space over which
that probability distribution is defined. It is useful to have a deterministic scoring rule comparing two points; norms form a natural class of examples.

Definition 11.81
.

We call a function
\mathsf{d}(\cdot,\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
a
distance-like deterministic scoring rule

if it satisfies the following three properties for all
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:

1.

Non-negative:
\mathsf{d}(u,v)\geq 0.

2.

Positive:
\mathsf{d}(u,v)=0
if and only if
u=v.

3.

Symmetric:
\mathsf{d}(u,v)=\mathsf{d}(v,u).

∎

Example 11.82
.

Let
\mathsf{d}(u,v)=\|u-v\|^{p}
for any norm
\|\cdot\|
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
p\in(0,\infty).
Then
\mathsf{d}(\cdot,\cdot)
is a distance-like scoring rule.
∎

Bibliography

The paper
[

constitutes a readable introduction to the study of

on the set of probability measures.

\mathsf{d}(z,u)=|z-u|
on

The

overviews the current state of the art
and reflects the growing use of these ideas.

An important consideration for sample-based metrics is their statistical consistency with empirical measures and the associated rates of convergence to their population-level limits. The paper
[
502
]
shows that the sample-averaged Wasserstein-
p
metric, for

\mathbb{E}[W_{p}(\nu,\nu^{n})]=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/d})
. On the other hand, the MMD with appropriate choices of the kernel only depends on the mean embedding of the measures in the RKHS and thus converges at the rate
\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/2})
, independent of the dimension of the data; see

Chapter 12
Supervised Learning

This chapter is concerned with the

supervised learning
task of approximating,
from data, a function
\psi^{\dagger}:D\to R,

D
the
domain
and
R
the
range
. Supervised learning refers
specifically to learning
\psi^{\dagger}
from data given
in the form of input–output pairs of
\psi^{\dagger}.

In this chapter we focus on the
regression

problem in which the domain
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and range
R\subseteq\mathbb{R}
are subsets
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathbb{R},
respectively; the methods we introduce are readily generalized
to the case where
R
is a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
for any integer
m
.

When the range of
\psi^{\dagger}
has finite cardinality the function
approximation problem is referred to as
classification
. Classification
played a central role in the historical development of supervised learning, and
is still one of its major uses in current applications of machine learning?
Here, however, we focus on regression: this is a task that arises naturally in the
context of learning forward maps for inverse problems and learning dynamical systems
for data assimilation; in particular learning emulators.
Our data assumption is as follows:

Data Assumption 12.1
.

Let
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
R\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be open sets, let
\psi^{\dagger}:D\to R,

let
\{\xi^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
be i.i.d. centered random variables taking values in
\mathbb{R}
, and let
\lambda\geq 0.

Data is available in the form

(12.1)

\displaystyle\Bigl\{(u^{(n)},y^{(n)})\Bigr\}_{n=1}^{N},\quad y^{(n)}=\psi^{\dagger}(u^{(n)})+\sqrt{\lambda}\xi^{(n)},

where the
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
are generated i.i.d. from (unknown)
probability density function
\Upsilon(u)
, supported on
D
, and independent of
\{\xi^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
.

The parameter
\lambda\geq 0
allows us to consider noiseless data (
\lambda=0)
and noisy data
(\lambda>0).
Since the goal is to determine
\psi^{\dagger}
we are concerned with function
approximation, and to that end we will study three approaches to parameterize and learn functions: neural networks (Section
12.2
), random features (Section
12.3
), and Gaussian processes (Section
12.4
).
We will concentrate on the noiseless case
\lambda=0
, but will briefly mention the noisy case
\lambda>0
in the context of random features and Gaussian processes.
Before considering these methods of function approximation we introduce
some notational conventions that will be useful in this and the following chapter.

12.1
Notational Conventions

Throughout this section
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an open set and
\Upsilon
is a
probability density function supported on
D
.
Given
\Upsilon
we may define a Hilbert space structure:

Definition 12.2
.

We let
H_{\Upsilon}
denote the Hilbert space
of real-valued functions on
D

with inner-product and induced norm

\langle\psi,\varphi\rangle_{H_{\Upsilon}}=\int_{D}\psi(u)\varphi(u)\Upsilon(u)\,du,\quad|\psi|_{H_{\Upsilon}}^{2}=\langle\psi,\psi\rangle_{H_{\Upsilon}}.

∎

Thus the induced norm on
H_{\Upsilon}
is given more explicitly by

(12.2)

|\psi|_{H_{\Upsilon}}=\biggl(\int_{D}\psi(u)^{2}\Upsilon(u)\,du\biggr)^{1/2}.

Definition 12.3
.

Given the data from Data Assumption
12.1

we define the
empirical measure

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{N}(u)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{u^{(n)}}(u).

∎

The probability density function

\Upsilon
is thus approximated by the empirical measure
\Upsilon^{N}

– see Theorem
11.34
.
We may then define an empirical approximation of
|\psi|_{H_{\Upsilon}}

by replacing
\Upsilon
by
\Upsilon^{N}
in (
12.2
) to obtain

(12.3)

|\psi|_{H_{\Upsilon^{\mbox{\tiny{{\rm N}}}}}}=\Biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\psi(u^{(n)})^{2}\Biggr)^{1/2}.

This does not define a norm on the vector space of functions from
D
into
\mathbb{R};

however it approximates the norm
|\psi|_{H_{\Upsilon}}
on that space.

12.2
Neural Networks

We define neural networks acting as real-valued
maps on finite-dimensional Euclidean space. We use neural networks
to perform regression from data.
For simplicity we work, throughout this section,
under Data Assumption
12.1
, considering only
the noise-free setting
\lambda=0.

To define a neural network we make the following
definition:

Definition 12.4
.

An
activation function
is a map
\sigma:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},
extended to
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{s}\to\mathbb{R}^{s}
pointwise:
\sigma(u)_{m}=\sigma(u_{m})
for
u=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{m},\ldots,u_{s})
for all
m\in\{1,\ldots,s\}.

∎

The activation function in the neural network construction that follows below is the only source of nonlinearity in the neural
network; it is thus essential to the ability of neural networks to effectively approximate nonlinear
\psi^{\dagger}.

Several commonly made choices of activation function are now defined:

Example 12.5
.

The
Rectified Linear Unit (ReLU)
activation function is
\sigma(u)={\rm max}(u,0).
The

Gaussian Error Linear Unit (GELU)

activation function is
\sigma(u)=uF(u)
, where
F
is the univariate standard Gaussian cumulative distribution function.
The
Scaled Exponential Linear Unit (SELU)
activation function is

(12.4)

\displaystyle\sigma(u)=\begin{cases}\kappa u,&\quad u>0,\\
\kappa\alpha\bigl(\exp(u)-1\bigr),&\quad u\leq 0\end{cases}

for fixed positive constants
\kappa
and
\alpha
.
The
Exponential Linear Unit (ELU)
activation function is obtained from
the SELU by setting
\kappa=1.

∎

A
neural network
is a
parametric family of functions
found by composing activation functions with affine functions.
We introduce parameter space
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{\theta}}
and decompose element
\theta\in\Theta

into
\theta=(\vartheta,\beta).
Then we may define

\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}
via the iteration

(12.5)

\displaystyle\psi^{(0)}(u;\vartheta)

\displaystyle=u,

(12.6)

\displaystyle\psi^{(\ell+1)}(u;\vartheta)

\displaystyle=\sigma\bigl(W_{\ell}\psi^{(\ell)}(u;\vartheta)+b_{\ell}\bigr),\quad\ell=0,\dots,L-1,

(12.7)

\displaystyle\psi(u;\theta)

\displaystyle=\beta^{\top}\psi^{(L)}(u;\vartheta).

Here, for
\ell\in\{0,\ldots,L\}
,
d_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}
, where
d_{0}=d
and the other integers are design choices. Then,
for
\ell\in\{0,\ldots,L-1\}
,
W_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\ell+1}\times d_{\ell}}
(the
weights
)
and
b_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\ell+1}},
(the
biases
), define

\vartheta;
and
\vartheta
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{L}}
(also weights) together
collectively define
\theta
(and implicitly
d_{\theta}\in\mathbb{N}
).
Note that
\psi^{(\ell)}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\ell}}.

The resulting function
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}
is known as a

(deep
1
1

1

Deep if
L
is large enough; often three or more is considered large in this context. Lower values are sometimes referred to as
shallow neural networks
.
) neural network
.

Remark 12.6
.

The reader will note that the concept of neural networks is readily
generalized to
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{q}
for any integer
q;

we concentrate on
q=1
for simplicity
of exposition only.
∎

12.2.1
Empirical Risk Minimization

By use of the data, a value of the parameter
\theta
defining the neural
network may be chosen so as to determine an approximation of
\psi^{\dagger}.

We first study how this might be undertaken by considering this problem where the data is the entire measure
\Upsilon
–
the population loss perspective.

Definition 12.7
.

Assume function
\psi^{\dagger}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is given. For fixed dimensions
\{d_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{L}
,
we define the
best neural network approximation
to
\psi^{\dagger}
by
\psi^{*}=\psi(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})
where

(12.8)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta),

and
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)
is the
risk

(12.9)

{\mathsf{J}}(\theta):=\frac{1}{2}|\psi^{\dagger}-\psi(\cdot;\theta)|_{H_{\Upsilon}}^{2}

∎

We now discuss what is done in practice to determine a neural network approximation of
\psi^{\dagger}
, given Data
Assumption
12.1
, rather than knowledge of
\Upsilon
itself. Definition
12.7
of best approximation requires
the probability density function
\Upsilon
and the function
\psi^{\dagger},
neither of which are known.
What we do have access to is the data set in Data Assumption
12.1
which contains
implicit information about both
\Upsilon
and
\psi^{\dagger}
and, in particular, defines the empirical
measure
\Upsilon^{N}(u)
in Definition
12.3
. It is then natural to choose the optimal parameter
\theta^{\star}

to minimize the
empirical risk

{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta):=\frac{1}{2}|\psi^{\dagger}-\psi(\cdot;\theta)|_{H_{\Upsilon^{\mbox{\tiny{{\rm N}}}}}}^{2}.

Notice that, since we have assumed that
\lambda=0
,

(12.10)

{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}|y^{(n)}-\psi(u^{(n)};\theta)|^{2}.

Thus in practice we specify the dimensions
\{d_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{L}
and then numerically estimate
\theta^{\star}
through
empirical risk minimization.

Minimizing (
12.10
) leads to an implementable strategy for function approximation.
The empirical risk is typically non-convex as a function of
\theta
,
with multiple saddle points and local minima. Robust software to perform the minimization via the use of
stochastic gradient descent (Chapter
15
) is readily
available. In practice
\theta^{\star}
is obtained using this software and, whilst the value obtained is
not expected to be the global minimizer of (
12.10
), empirical evidence suggests that the
values obtained perform well at making the loss function
{\mathsf{J}}^{N}
in (
12.10
) close to optimal.
We write
\psi^{\star}(u):=\psi(u;\theta^{\star}).

Remark 12.8
.

We have derived the loss function
{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\cdot)
in (
12.10
) under Data Assumption
12.1

with
\lambda=0
. However it may also be derived under the more general assumption
that data set
(u^{(n)},y^{(n)})
is drawn i.i.d. from (unknown) probability measure
\pi
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}.

Examples of such measures may be found from Data Assumption
12.1
for any
\lambda\geq 0,
but the setting is much more
general. The risk may then be written as

{\mathsf{J}}(\theta):=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(u,y)\sim\pi}|y-\psi(u;\theta)|^{2}

and approximated empirically to again obtain (
12.10
).
∎

Remark 12.9
.

We derived (
12.10
) by approximating (
12.9
) empirically. If
the objective functional
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)
in (
12.9
) is viewed as a function of
\psi
,
rather than
\theta
, then we say that the optimization problem is nonparametric.
It is interesting to note that this problem is convex in
\psi.
This fact is often used to
motivate the empirical observation that the parametric optimization problem (
12.10
)
becomes simpler when over-parameterized.
∎

12.2.2
Universal Approximation

We now ask whether, by choosing sufficiently large
dimensions
\{d_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{L}
and choosing optimal parameters,
we can determine neural networks that approximate a given function to
arbitrary accuracy. There is a deep
universal approximation

theory for neural networks that, in broad terms, answers this question
in the affirmative. We state a typical theorem of this type for a two-layer
neural network. Citations to proof of the stated theorem, and to other relevant literature, may be found in
the bibliography Section
12.6
.

In this and following sections it is instructive to write the two-layer neural
network in another form. We set
L=1
, and note that integer
d_{1}
must be chosen
to specify the neural network. For
i=1,\ldots,d_{1}
, we let
w^{(i)}

denote the
d_{1}
rows of
W_{0}
,
b^{(i)}
the
d_{1}

components of
b_{0}
and
\beta_{i}
the
d_{1}
components of
\beta.

We may also define
\vartheta^{(i)}=(w^{(i)},b^{(i)})
, noting that
\vartheta

is defined by the concatenation of the
\vartheta^{(i)}
over
i=1,\ldots,d_{1}
.
Then we may write the neural network (
12.5
) as

(12.11a)

\displaystyle\psi(u;\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{1}}\beta_{i}\varphi(u;\vartheta^{(i)}),

(12.11b)

\displaystyle\varphi(u;\vartheta^{(i)})

\displaystyle=\sigma\bigl(\langle w^{(i)},u\rangle+b^{(i)}\bigr).

Theorem 12.10
.

Let
\sigma\in C(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
and assume that
\sigma
is not a polynomial. Consider the neural network (
12.5
) with
L=1,

which may then be written as (
12.11
).
Assume we are given any function
\psi^{\dagger}\in C(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
, any compact set
\mathsf{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
\varepsilon>0.
Then there is
integer
d_{1}
, and parameter
\theta=(W_{0},b_{0},\beta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
such that

\sup_{u\in\mathsf{B}}|\psi^{\dagger}(u)-\psi(u;\theta)|<\varepsilon.

12.3
Random Features

To motivate the idea of random features we return to the two-layer neural network
as defined in (
12.11
). Recall that
\theta=(\vartheta,\beta)
where

\vartheta=(W_{0},b_{0})
and where matrix
W_{0}
has rows
\{w^{(i)}\}
and vector
b_{0}

has components
\{b^{(i)}\}
, for
i=1,\ldots,d_{1}.
Now imagine that we fix
\vartheta
and
view the function approximation as being parameterized only by
\beta:

(12.12a)

\displaystyle\psi(u;\vartheta,\beta)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{1}}\beta_{i}\varphi(u;\vartheta^{(i)}),

(12.12b)

\displaystyle\varphi(u;\vartheta^{(i)})

\displaystyle=\sigma\bigl(\langle w^{(i)},u\rangle+b^{(i)}\bigr).

The functions
\varphi_{i}(\cdot):=\varphi(\cdot,\vartheta^{(i)})
are often termed
features
.

\displaystyle\beta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}}\frac{1}{2}|\psi^{\dagger}-\psi(\cdot;\vartheta,\beta)|_{H_{\Upsilon^{\mbox{\tiny{{\rm N}}}}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}}\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}|y^{(n)}-\psi(u^{(n)};\vartheta,\beta)|^{2}

and
\psi^{\star}(u):=\psi(u;\vartheta,\beta^{\star}).
In contrast to full optimization over
\theta

discussed in the previous section,
this leads to a convex, indeed quadratic, optimization problem that can be readily solved.
We are seeking the best solution in the linear span of the features.

So far we have simply fixed
\vartheta;
we have not discussed how to choose it.
One natural approach is to simply pick
\vartheta
at random from some
probability measure. Indeed in (
12.12
) we may pick the pair

(w^{(i)},b^{(i)})
i.i.d. at random. It is intuitive that working
with an i.i.d. set of random functions will increase expressivity.
We now take this idea and abstract it, not limiting ourselves
to the specific features defined by the two-layer neural network.
To realize this construction we consider
q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\vartheta}})

and define
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{\vartheta}}\to\mathbb{R}
. We then
choose
m\in\mathbb{N}
and set

(12.13)

\psi(u;\beta):=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}\varphi(u;\vartheta_{i}),\quad\vartheta_{i}\sim q,\,\text{i.i.d.}

We have now defined i.i.d.
random features

\varphi_{i}(\cdot):=\varphi(\cdot;\vartheta_{i})
for
i=1,\ldots,m.

Remark 12.11
.

The reader will note that the concept of random features is readily
generalized to
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{q}
for any integer
q,

as are neural networks – see Remark
12.6
.
∎

Analogous to the situation for the neural network in (
12.9
), the idealized approach to
determining
\beta
is to minimize the
risk

(12.14)

{\mathsf{J}}(\beta):=\frac{1}{2}|\psi^{\dagger}-\psi(\cdot;\beta)|_{H_{\Upsilon}}^{2}.

Again analogously to our discussion of neural networks, we may employ empirical risk minimization to determine vector
\beta
in (
12.13
). In addition we now also introduce a regularization term
and consider the regularized quadratic empirical loss function

(12.15)

{\mathsf{J}}^{N,\mathsf{reg}}(\beta):=\frac{1}{2}|\psi^{\dagger}-\psi(\cdot;\beta)|_{H_{\Upsilon^{\mbox{\tiny{{\rm N}}}}}}^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{\mathsf{reg}}}{2N}|\beta|^{2}.

Informally this recovers
{\mathsf{J}}(\beta)
from (
12.14
) in the limit
N\to\infty.

Here
\lambda_{\mathsf{reg}}>0
is the regularization parameter. Although we have considered noiseless data
\lambda=0,

the specific regularization we use here arises naturally in the context of noisy data
\xi^{(n)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
in (
12.1
)
– see Subsection
12.4.4
.

The optimal choice
\beta
is defined by minimizing
{\mathsf{J}}^{N,\mathsf{reg}}(\beta).
Using (
12.3
) this
minimization problem may be written as

(12.16a)

\displaystyle\beta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}{\mathsf{J}}^{N,\mathsf{reg}}(\beta),

(12.16b)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{N,\mathsf{reg}}(\beta)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}|y^{(n)}-\psi(u^{(n)};\beta)|^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{\mathsf{reg}}}{2N}|\beta|^{2}.

Similarly to the previous section, in which we studied neural networks,
we write
\psi^{\star}(u):=\psi(u;\beta^{\star}).

The optimization problem (
12.16
) is quadratic. Furthermore,
since
\lambda_{\mathsf{reg}}>0
, the system of linear equations is
guaranteed to have a unique solution. This we now prove.

Theorem 12.12
.

Assume that
\lambda_{\mathsf{reg}}>0
.
Then the linear system for
\beta^{\star}
defined by the optimization problem
(
12.16
) takes the form
B\beta^{\star}=r
where
B
is strictly
positive-definite.

Proof.

The optimization problem in
\beta
is quadratic, and hence the minimizer

\beta^{\star}
solves the linear system

B\beta^{\star}=r
,
where

B_{ij}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\varphi(u^{(n)};\vartheta^{(i)})\varphi(u^{(n)};\vartheta^{(j)})+\frac{\lambda_{\mathsf{reg}}}{N}\delta_{ij},\qquad r_{i}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}y^{(n)}\varphi(u^{(n)};\vartheta^{(i)}).

For each
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
define
\varphi(u)\in{\mathbb{R}^{m}}
to be
the vector with
i
-th entry
\varphi(u;\vartheta_{i})
.
From this it follows that

\langle z,Bz\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon^{N}}|\langle\varphi(u),z\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}|^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{\mathsf{reg}}}{N}|z|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\geq\frac{\lambda_{\mathsf{reg}}}{N}|z|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}.

Thus symmetric matrix
B
is strictly positive definite since we have shown that all eigenvalues are bounded below by
\frac{\lambda_{\mathsf{reg}}}{N}>0.

∎

Example 12.13
.

Random Fourier features take the form

\varphi(u;\vartheta)=\cos\bigl(\langle\omega,u\rangle+b\bigr)

where
\vartheta=(\omega,b)
is chosen at random.
Note that this is precisely the form of feature arising in the
two-layer neural network (
12.12
), with activation function chosen as
the cosine function. It is natural to take
b\sim U[0,2\pi]
,
and we assume
\omega\sim W,
for some
W\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

We assume also that
\omega\perp\!\!\!\!\perp b.

∎

Example 12.14
.

Each
\varphi(\cdot;\vartheta_{i})
may be chosen as a neural network, for example, with the parameters of that
neural network chosen i.i.d. at random from a given distribution
q
.
∎

The spatial correlation in the random features
is a natural object to study. To this end, we introduce kernel (recall Definition
11.19
)

(12.17)

\displaystyle c(u,u^{\prime}):=\mathbb{E}^{q}\bigl[\varphi(u;\vartheta)\varphi(u^{\prime};\vartheta)\bigr],

where expectation is over
\vartheta\sim q.

Example 12.15
.

Consider Example
12.13
. We note that

\displaystyle c(u,u^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}I(\omega)W(\omega)\,d\omega,

\displaystyle I(\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos\bigl(\langle\omega,u\rangle+b\bigr)\cos\bigl(\langle\omega,u^{\prime}\rangle+b\bigr)\,db

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cos\bigl(\langle\omega,u-u^{\prime}\rangle\bigr).

Thus the resulting kernel depends only on

u-u^{\prime}:c(u,u^{\prime})=\mathfrak{c}(u-u^{\prime})
for some
\mathfrak{c}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}.

We say that the kernel is

stationary.
∎

The attraction of the random features
approach to supervised learning is that it leads to a quadratic
finite-dimensional optimization problem over the
m
random features.
In the next section we discuss Gaussian process regression which also
leads to quadratic finite-dimensional optimization problem, but with dimension

N
, the number of data points. Furthermore, rather than working in
a set of random features, Gaussian process regression works in the
span of the covariance functions, with one argument evaluated at a data point.
The random features approach may be viewed as a Monte Carlo approximation
of Gaussian process regression.

12.4
Gaussian Processes

In this section we describe Gaussian process regression.
In Subsection
12.4.1
we introduce the elements needed to define Gaussian process regression,
namely the notions of kernel (Definition
11.19
) and RKHS (Definition
11.21
).
We study the infinite-dimensional regression problem in Subsection
12.4.2
,
showing how it is, in fact, a finite-dimensional problem in Subsection
12.4.3
. We conclude by
connecting to random features methodology in Subsection
12.4.4
.

12.4.1
Kernels and RKHSs

In the study of random features based regression, the kernel given by (
12.17
) is a byproduct of the random features.
In Gaussian process regression it is the starting point for defining the regression methodology.
In the theory of Gaussian process regression, kernels are used to specify the covariance function of a Gaussian process model; the covariance function quantifies spatial correlations of the process and determines a choice of RKHS in which to formulate the regression problem. In this subsection we show, under mild assumptions, an explicit construction of the RKHS associated with the covariance function of a Gaussian process on a compact domain. In the following Subsection
12.4.2
we formulate and study Gaussian process regression in this RKHS.

Let
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be compact and let
c
be a symmetric, positive definite and
continuous kernel, interpreted as the covariance function of a Gaussian process. The integral operator
\mathcal{C}:L^{2}(D)\to L^{2}(D)
given by

(12.18)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C}\varphi)(u)=\int_{D}c(u,u^{\prime})\varphi(u^{\prime})\,du^{\prime},\qquad\varphi\in L^{2}(D),

is called the
covariance operator
with kernel
c.
As discussed in the bibliography Section
12.6
,
our assumptions on
c
imply that the covariance operator
admits eigenpairs
\{\sigma_{i},\varphi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
satisfying
\mathcal{C}\varphi_{i}=\sigma_{i}\varphi_{i},
where the eigenvalues
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
are non-negative, and, without loss of generality, decreasingly ordered; and where the eigenfunctions
\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(D)
. Furthermore, Mercer’s theorem (see the bibliography Section
12.6
) ensures that the eigenfunctions corresponding to non-zero eigenvalues are continuous on
D
(which implies that pointwise evaluations are well defined) and that the covariance function can be represented using the eigenpairs of
\mathcal{C}
as follows:

(12.19)

c(u,u^{\prime})=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{i}\varphi_{i}(u)\varphi_{i}(u^{\prime}),\qquad u,u^{\prime}\in D,

where the convergence is absolute and uniform. This representation will be repeatedly used in what follows. For ease of presentation, we will restrict attention to kernels for which
\mathcal{C}
is
injective, so that
\sigma_{i}>0
for every
i\geq 1
.

Next, we seek to explicitly characterize the RKHS (Definition
11.21
) corresponding to kernel
c
using again the eigenpairs of
\mathcal{C}.
To that end, let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}}
denote the inner product in
L^{2}(D),
and define

{\mathcal{K}}:=\biggl\{f\in L^{2}(D):\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\langle f,\varphi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\sigma_{i}}<\infty\biggr\}.

The set
{\mathcal{K}}
equipped with inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{{\mathcal{K}}}
as now defined is a Hilbert space:

\langle f,g\rangle_{{\mathcal{K}}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\langle f,\varphi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}}\langle g,\varphi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}}}{\sigma_{i}},\qquad f,g\in{\mathcal{K}}.

The following proposition shows that
{\mathcal{K}}
is an RKHS with reproducing kernel
c.

Proposition 12.16
.

For
f\in{\mathcal{K}}
and
u\in D,
it holds that

(12.20)

\langle f,c(u,\cdot)\rangle_{{\mathcal{K}}}=f(u).

Consequently,
{\mathcal{K}}
is an RKHS with reproducing kernel
c.

Proof.

Note that, using (
12.19
), we have

(12.21)

\langle c(u,\cdot),\varphi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}(u)\langle\varphi_{j},\varphi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}}=\sigma_{i}\varphi_{i}(u),

where the last equality follows since
(\varphi_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
are
orthonormal in
L^{2}(D)
. It follows from (
12.21
) and
(
12.19
) that

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\langle c(u,\cdot),\varphi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\sigma_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{i}\varphi_{i}(u)^{2}=c(u,u)<\infty.

Hence
c(u,\cdot)\in{\mathcal{K}}
and

\|c(u,\cdot)\|_{{\mathcal{K}}}^{2}=c(u,u)
. Furthermore, by the
Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\langle f,\varphi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}}\varphi_{i}(u)}}\right|\leq\|f\|_{{\mathcal{K}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{i}\varphi_{i}(u)^{2}}}\right)^{1/2}=\|f\|_{{\mathcal{K}}}c(u,u)^{1/2}.

Consequently, the
L^{2}
-expansion of
f
converges absolutely at every

u\in D
and defines a pointwise representative of
f
, also denoted
by
f
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\langle f,c(u,\cdot)\rangle_{{\mathcal{K}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\langle f,\varphi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}}\langle c(u,\cdot),\varphi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}}}{\sigma_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\langle f,\varphi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}}\varphi_{i}(u)=f(u).

∎

An important example of a kernel widely used in Gaussian process regression is the
Gaussian kernel
given by

(12.22)

c(u,u^{\prime}):=\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2}|u-u^{\prime}|^{2}\Bigr),\qquad u,u^{\prime}\in D.

The following theorem provides a universal approximation result for Gaussian kernels, analogous to Theorem
12.10
for neural networks.

Theorem 12.17
.

Let
{\mathcal{K}}
be the RKHS of a Gaussian kernel on a compact domain
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Assume we are given any function
\psi^{\dagger}\in C(D,\mathbb{R})
and
\varepsilon>0.
Then there exists
\psi\in{\mathcal{K}}
such that

\sup_{u\in D}|\psi^{\dagger}(u)-\psi(u)|<\varepsilon.

A reference containing proof of this result, together with additional examples of kernels satisfying a similar universal
approximation property, can be found in the bibliography Section
12.6
.

Remark 12.18
.

Recall that the concepts of neural networks and random features are readily
generalized to
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{q}
for any integer
q,

– see Remarks
12.6
and
12.11
. Generalizing Gaussian processes
requires a significant conceptual leap, in that the kernel
c
from Definition

11.19
becomes matrix valued:
c:D\times D\to\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}.

∎

12.4.2
Regression

We work under Data Assumption
12.1
in the setting
\lambda>0.

We assume that
{\mathcal{K}}
is an RKHS with reproducing kernel
c
, as constructed in the previous subsection,
and seek to find approximation to
\psi^{\dagger}
, function
\psi^{\star}
, through the following minimization problem:

(12.23a)

\displaystyle\psi^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\psi\in{\mathcal{K}}}{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\psi),

(12.23b)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\psi)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}|y^{(n)}-\psi(u^{(n)})|^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2N}\|\psi\|_{{\mathcal{K}}}^{2}.

This is known as Gaussian process regression.
In the limit
N\to\infty
this recovers, informally, the problem

(12.24a)

\displaystyle\psi^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\psi\in{\mathcal{K}}}{\mathsf{J}}(\psi),

(12.24b)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\psi)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}|\psi^{\dagger}-\psi|_{H_{\Upsilon}}^{2}.

In contrast to the previous sections on neural network and random features based
regression, in which the loss functions were defined over finite-dimensional parameter spaces, Gaussian process regression leads to a loss defined over the infinite-dimensional space
{\mathcal{K}}.

However, remarkably, the resulting optimization problem reduces to a finite-dimensional problem of
dimension defined by the size of the data set. This we now show.

12.4.3
The Representer Theorem

The infinite-dimensional optimization problem (
12.23
)
over
{\mathcal{K}}
has the following remarkable property, which
is often referred to as the

representer theorem
, and demonstrates
that the optimization problem is intrinsically finite dimensional:

Theorem 12.19
.

Function
\psi^{\star}
solving (
12.23
) has the form

(12.25)

\displaystyle\psi^{\star}(u)=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\alpha^{\star}_{n}c(u,u^{(n)}).

Furthermore, the coefficients
\alpha^{\star}
solve the
following quadratic minimization problem:

\displaystyle\alpha^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{N}}\mathsf{A}^{N}(\alpha),

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}^{N}(\alpha)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{r=1}^{N}\Big|y^{(r)}-\sum_{n=1}^{N}\alpha_{n}c(u^{(r)},u^{(n)})\Big|^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2N}\sum_{n,r=1}^{N}\alpha_{n}\alpha_{r}c(u^{(r)},u^{(n)}).

Remark 12.20
.

In the context of supervised learning, notice that the preceding
theorem shows that the optimization problem (
12.23
) is in fact
finite dimensional in nature, despite its infinite-dimensional
formulation. Note, furthermore, the key fact that solution to the
optimization problem is defined entirely by
knowledge of the kernel

c
and by the data
\Bigl\{(u^{(n)},y^{(n)})\Bigr\}_{n=1}^{N}.

∎

Proof of Theorem
12.19
.

Using the reproducing property (
12.20
), the objective function
{\mathsf{J}}^{N}
in (
12.23
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle N{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{N}|y^{(n)}-\psi(u^{(n)})|^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\psi\|_{{\mathcal{K}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{N}|y^{(n)}-\langle c(\cdot,u^{(n)}),\psi\rangle_{{\mathcal{K}}}|^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\psi\|_{{\mathcal{K}}}^{2}\,.

Writing
{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\psi+h)={\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\psi)+\langle D{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\psi),h\rangle_{{\mathcal{K}}}+\mathcal{O}(\|h\|_{{\mathcal{K}}}^{2})

identifies the variational derivative
D{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\psi)
of
{\mathsf{J}}^{N}
, evaluated at any point
\psi.
Setting this derivative to zero at minimizer
\psi=\psi^{\star}
gives, for
u\in D,

\displaystyle\lambda\psi^{\star}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bigl(y^{(n)}-\langle c(\cdot,u^{(n)}),\psi^{\star}\rangle_{{\mathcal{K}}}\bigr)c(u,u^{(n)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bigl(y^{(n)}-\psi^{\star}(u^{(n)})\bigr)c(u,u^{(n)}).

This shows that
\psi^{\star}
is in the linear
span of
\{c(\cdot,u^{(n)})\}_{n=1}^{N},
establishing the first part of the result.

For the second part, let

{\mathcal{K}}^{N}=\Bigl\{\psi\in{\mathcal{K}}:\psi=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\alpha_{n}c(\cdot,u^{(n)}),\quad\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{R}\,\,\forall n\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\Bigr\}

and define the optimization problem

(12.26a)

\displaystyle\psi^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\psi\in{\mathcal{K}}^{N}}{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\psi),

(12.26b)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\psi)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}|y^{(n)}-\psi(u^{(n)})|^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2N}\|\psi\|_{{\mathcal{K}}}^{2}.

Because we have established that (
12.25
) holds for the minimizer

\psi^{\star}
of (
12.23
), it follows that
\psi^{\star}
will also be the minimizer
of (
12.26
).
Finally, using the fact that
\psi\in{\mathcal{K}}^{N}
, and using the identities (
12.19
) and (
12.21
), it follows that

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{{\mathcal{K}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sigma_{i}}\Bigl\langle\sum_{n=1}^{N}\alpha_{n}c(\cdot,u^{(n)}),\varphi_{i}\Bigr\rangle_{L^{2}}\Bigl\langle\sum_{r=1}^{N}\alpha_{r}c(\cdot,u^{(r)}),\varphi_{i}\Bigr\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sigma_{i}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\alpha_{n}\sigma_{i}\varphi_{i}(u^{(n)})\sum_{r=1}^{N}\alpha_{r}\sigma_{i}\varphi_{i}(u^{(r)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,r=1}^{N}\alpha_{n}\alpha_{r}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{i}\varphi_{i}(u^{(n)})\varphi_{i}(u^{(r)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,r=1}^{N}\alpha_{n}\alpha_{r}c(u^{(n)},u^{(r)}),

concluding the proof.
∎

We define
C\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
to be the matrix with entries

C_{nr}=c(u^{(n)},u^{(r)})
. We assume that
C
is invertible and denote its
inverse by
S
, with entries
S_{nr}
. This assumption holds, for
example, if
c
is strictly positive definite and the input points

u^{(n)}
,
n=1,\ldots,N
, are pairwise distinct.
Then we have:

Corollary 12.21
.

If
p^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
is vector with entries
p^{\star}_{n}=\psi^{\star}(u^{(n)}),
then

p^{\star}=C\alpha^{\star}
and

\displaystyle p^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{p\in\mathbb{R}^{N}}{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(p),

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(p)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}|y^{(n)}-p_{n}|^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2N}\sum_{n,r=1}^{N}p_{n}p_{r}S_{n,r}.

12.4.4
Connection to Random Features

The minimization problems (
12.16
), from random feature
regression, and (
12.23
), from Gaussian process regression, are
closely connected. Given
\vartheta_{i}\sim q
i.i.d., define the empirical
kernel

c_{m}(u,u^{\prime}):=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\varphi(u;\vartheta_{i})\varphi(u^{\prime};\vartheta_{i}).

Then

\mathbb{E}[c_{m}(u,u^{\prime})]=c(u,u^{\prime}),

so that
c_{m}
is a Monte Carlo approximation of the
kernel
c
in (
12.17
).

Let
{\mathcal{K}}_{m}
denote the RKHS with reproducing kernel
c_{m}
. Its elements
have representations of the form

\psi(u;\beta)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}\varphi(u;\vartheta_{i}),

with

\|\psi\|_{{\mathcal{K}}_{m}}^{2}=\inf\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{|\beta|^{2}:\psi(\cdot)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}\varphi(\cdot;\vartheta_{i})}}\right\}.

Replacing
{\mathcal{K}}
by
{\mathcal{K}}_{m}
in (
12.23
) therefore reduces the
optimization problem to

\min_{\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|y^{(n)}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}\varphi(u^{(n)};\vartheta_{i})}}\right|^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2N}|\beta|^{2}}}\right\}.

Upon setting
\widetilde{\beta}=\beta/\sqrt{m}
, this agrees with
(
12.16
) with
\lambda_{\mathsf{reg}}=m\lambda
. Thus random feature
regression may be viewed as a finite-rank Monte Carlo approximation of
Gaussian process regression.

12.5
Approximation Properties

Theorem
12.10
demonstrates that two-layer neural networks
are, in principle, able to approximate a given continuous function arbitrarily well;
related results exist for neural networks with more than two layers and are cited
in the bibliography Section
12.6
.
Theorem
12.17
provides a similar result for approximation
using Gaussian kernels; and in view of the Representer Theorem
12.19

this theorem suggests that Gaussian process regression also leads to
a methodology that can approximate a given continuous function arbitrarily well;
indeed such results exist, with rates, for many different kernels and are cited
in the bibliography Section
12.6
. In addition, there are also density results
for random features approximation, also cited in the bibliography Section
12.6
.

To be maximally useful, these results on approximation theory need to be tied to
the outcome of the optimizations defining parameter choices found in practice.
For Gaussian process regression the situation is straightforward since the parameter optimization
problem is quadratic with known solution; and indeed there is substantial development
of approximation theory concerning this known solution.
The same is true for approximation using random features, for which explicit solution of the
quadratic optimization problem is also known, and which may be viewed as a form of
Gaussian process regression in a space defined by the span of the random features.
The situation for neural networks, when parameters are trained
on an empirical risk function, is considerably less well-developed.
Statistical learning theory
addresses this question by first studying approximation for minimizers of the risk, and then studying the
effect of empiricalization. However, it still leaves open the question of how typical optimizers
used to learn neural networks, such as those based on
stochastic gradient descent (see Chapter
15
), perform in practice.

12.6
Bibliography

The subject of neural network function approximation, the
core task of supervised learning,
is overviewed in
[
147
]
.
Four key universal approximation theorems for neural networks
were published in 1989:

[
103
,
136
,
182
,
451
]
;
in particular, Theorem
12.10
is stated
and proved, by synthesizing this work, in
[
