, Theorem 3.1]
.
For introductions to statistical learning theory
see the texts
[
231
,
484
]
.

The use of random features was popularized as a methodology,
and analyzed rigorously, in the collection of papers

[
395
,
396
,
397
]
.
We have articulated a specific link between neural networks
and random features; a related concept underpins the subject of the

neural tangent kernel

[
261
]
.

Gaussian processes are described in
[
508
]
; in that text the mean function and covariance function characterize the Gaussian probability measure on function
space. In
[
74
]
Gaussian probability measure on separable Banach
space
X
is defined by asking that all linear functionals are Gaussian. Subsequently the mean, viewed as a point in
X
, and the covariance
operator, mapping
X^{*}
into
X
, may be defined. In this Gaussian measure perspective, the RKHS is referred to as the Cameron–Martin space. Section 4 of the lecture notes

[
220
]
provides a concise readable introduction to the subject of Gaussian
measure on separable Banach space. Kernel-based methods more generally are described in

[
370
]
. We refer to
[
346
]
for a general theory and numerous examples of kernels with the universal approximation property, including the result for squared exponential kernels in Theorem
12.17
.
Error estimates for interpolation
using Gaussian processes are developed in
[
504
]
.
The link between regression and Bayesian inversion with
Gaussian process priors is developed in depth in

[
133
,
495
]
.
Mercer’s theorem is established in
[
343
]
and it can be now found in numerous textbooks; see for example
[
154
]
, which also contains additional background on the spectral decomposition of the covariance operator used in Subsection
12.4.1
, and
[
457
]
in the context of uncertainty quantification. In RKHS theory, the spectral decomposition characterizes the RKHS of symmetric, positive definite continuous kernels, as discussed in this chapter. Mercer’s theorem is also useful in the theory of integral equations and in the study of stochastic processes, where it underpins the derivation of Karhunen–Loève expansions
[
380
]
.

The minimization problem (
12.23
) may be derived by
application of a generalization of Bayes Theorem

1.3
,
2
2

2

Note that in this interpretation the unknown on which we seek
a posterior distribution is now
\psi\in L^{2}(D)

(whereas in Bayes Theorem
1.3

it was
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
).

from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
L^{2}(D)
, and then a generalization of the notion
of MAP estimator – Definition
1.10
–
also from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
L^{2}(D).
The Bayesian inverse problem is defined
as follows. We place as prior on
\psi
a
Gaussian random field with mean zero and non-negative definite continuous
covariance function
c(u,u^{\prime});

this covariance function can be equivalently seen as specifying a Gaussian measure
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathcal{C})
in
L^{2}(D),
where
\mathcal{C}
is the covariance
operator given by (
12.18
).
The likelihood
is defined by assuming noisy data (
\lambda>0
) in (
12.1
),
where
\xi^{(n)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
i.i.d. .
Then the negative log-likelihood is given by

\frac{1}{2\lambda}\sum_{n=1}^{N}|y^{(n)}-\psi(u^{(n)})|^{2}.

Because the prior on
\psi
is Gaussian and the log-likelihood is
quadratic in
\psi
, the generalization of
Bayes Theorem
1.3
shows that the
posterior is conjugate to the prior
and is Gaussian. The posterior mean,
which is also a form of MAP estimator,
then satisfies (
12.23
). We remark that minimization
of (
12.16
) also
has a similar interpretation as a MAP estimator with respect to
a zero-mean Gaussian process with covariance
function

\frac{m\lambda}{\lambda_{\mathsf{reg}}}c_{m}(u,u^{\prime}).

In particular, when
\lambda_{\mathsf{reg}}=m\lambda
, this covariance function is

c_{m}
, the Monte Carlo approximation of
c
introduced in
Subsection
12.4.4
. A definition of MAP estimator in infinite dimensions
may be found in
[
138
]
; see also
[
297
,
284
,
289
,
35
]
for related discussion.

The use of various machine-learning-inspired approximation
methods in the solution of PDEs may be found in

[
398
,
359
,
119
]
.
In particular, these papers address, respectively, the use of neural networks, random features and
Gaussian processes to solve PDEs.

Chapter 13
Unsupervised Learning and Generative Modeling

This chapter is concerned with the subject of

unsupervised learning
.
The following data assumption is made:

Data Assumption 13.1
.

We have available data in the form

(13.1)

\displaystyle U:=\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N},

assumed to be drawn i.i.d. from (unknown)
probability density function
\Upsilon
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Given data of this form there are numerous questions one might ask.
One widely studied problem is that of detecting

clustering
within the data; we will not
pursue this important topic because it is not of direct relevance
to the solution of inverse problems and
data assimilation, our focus in these notes.
Here we pursue the problem of
generative modeling
:
we model
\Upsilon
and/or generate new approximate samples from it,
given only the data
U.

Machine learning ideas based on generative modeling are useful
for both inverse problems and
data assimilation.

Data Assumption
13.1
should be compared with the

supervised learning

Data Assumption
12.1
(or the generalization discussed
in Remark
12.8
), where we are given input-output pairs,
linked through an unknown function; our focus in that setting is on learning
about that
function
. Under the unsupervised learning Data Assumption
13.1

our focus is on learning about a
probability measure
underlying the data.

Given Data Assumption
13.1
we have access to the
empirical measure (see Definition
12.3
)

(13.2)

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{N}(u)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{u^{(n)}}(u).

For any probability measure
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, recall the notation

\mathfrak{p}(f):=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\mathfrak{p}}f(u)
.
Recall also the metric

\mathsf{d_{rand}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})=\sup_{|f|_{\infty}\leq 1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho(f)-\rho^{\prime}(f)}}\right)^{2}}}\right]}}\right|^{1/2},

on the space of random probability measures, from Subsection
11.1.6
.
Theorem
11.34
shows that
\mathsf{d_{rand}}(\Upsilon,\Upsilon^{N})^{2}=\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{N})
.
This highlights that, for large
N
and on average with respect to the i.i.d. sampling underlying the data,
the empirical measure is close to the true measure we wish to learn about.

We conclude this introductory section
by illustrating the idea of generative modeling with two examples.

Example 13.2
.

A natural starting point for generative modeling is to discuss

density estimation
. This methodology
seeks to approximate
\Upsilon(u)
, from within some class of densities, on the
basis of the data summarized in
\Upsilon^{N}(u).

Some approximations to
\Upsilon
within such classes can be used
to generate new (approximate) samples. An example is when the approximation is a
Gaussian mixture.
However, the goal of density estimation is not always generative modeling:
it may be to have a smooth density which can be differentiated or otherwise manipulated
in a way that
\Upsilon^{N}(u)
cannot.
∎

Example 13.3
.

We introduce the
measure transport
task of determining
function
g:\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and probability density function
\zeta
on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}

such that if
z\sim\zeta
, then (approximately)
g(z)\sim\Upsilon.
Thus the perfect pair

g,\zeta
would satisfy
g_{\sharp}\zeta=\Upsilon;
recall that
g_{\sharp}
denotes
the pushforward operation under function
g
.
Measure
\zeta
is chosen to be easy to sample from; a common setting is to assume that

\zeta
is Gaussian. Given
\zeta
, the task of identifying
g
must be undertaken empirically, since only
\Upsilon^{N}
is available, not
\Upsilon.

It is also possible to study relaxations of this problem in which a specified measure
\zeta
is convolved with a specified Gaussian whose mean is function
g
;
g
is then chosen so that
this convolution approximates
\Upsilon.

∎

In Section
13.1
we describe the subject of
density estimation, introduced in
Example
13.2
, and taking a particular perspective on it which links to several
themes arising in other places in the notes. In the remainder of the chapter
we study variants on the idea of measure transport
illustrated in Example
13.3
.
All the methods connect the target measure
\Upsilon
, which we
wish to sample, and defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, to a measure on a latent
space
\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}.

In Section
13.2
we study a general approach to measure transport akin to the density estimation approach studied in Section
13.1
; typically these methods employ
d_{z}=d.
Normalizing flows are studied in Section
13.3
.
The choice
d_{z}\neq d
is possible here too. But we study a continuum limit, using
neural ODEs, where the methodology has at its core an invertible mapping with
d_{z}=d.
In Section
13.4
we study score-based approaches that build on Langevin sampling. Sections
13.5
and
13.6

introduce autoencoders and variational autoencoders respectively,
where an approximate inverse for
g
is sought, although typically

d_{z}<d
so caution is required to define this carefully.
Generative adversarial networks
(GANs) are introduced in Section
13.7
, applicable with
d_{z}\neq d.

13.1
Density Estimation

Recall notation
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for the set of all probability density
functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Consider the problem of estimating the probability density function
underlying data
U:=\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
given in Data Assumption
13.1
.
We seek to solve the problem by finding a probability density
function from
a tractable class of probability density functions
\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
for the purpose of computations and for the
purpose of revealing an explicit, interpretable form. To be concrete we
assume that, for some parameter set
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
,

\mathcal{Q}=\bigl\{q(\cdot\,;\theta)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):\theta\in\Theta\bigr\}.

We start by formulating the problem of density estimation as one of finding

\mathbb{P}(\theta|U)
by applying Bayesian inference, the methodological
approach to inversion introduced in Chapter
1
. If we place prior
\mathbb{P}(\theta)
on unknown parameter
\theta,
then, noticing that
\mathbb{P}(U|\theta)=\prod_{n=1}^{N}\,q(u^{(n)};\theta)
, we obtain from Theorem
1.3
that the posterior on
\theta|U
is given by

(13.3)

\mathbb{P}(\theta|U)\propto\prod_{n=1}^{N}\,q(u^{(n)};\theta)\mathbb{P}(\theta).

If we seek a maximum
a posteriori
(MAP) estimator for
\theta,

then, following the developments in Subsection
1.2.1
,

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm{MAP}}}}^{N}(\theta),

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm{MAP}}}}^{N}(\theta)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,\log q(u^{(n)};\theta)-\log\mathbb{P}(\theta).

The first term in the definition of
{\mathsf{J}}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm{MAP}}}}^{N}(\cdot)
is a specific
instance of the loss function (
1.9
),
and the second is the regularizer (
1.10
).
If we drop the regularizer (see Remark
1.12
; we are then using a flat prior)
then we obtain the maximum likelihood estimation (MLE)
problem

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm{MLE}}}}^{N}(\theta),

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm{MLE}}}}^{N}(\theta)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,\log q(u^{(n)};\theta).

This MLE may also be derived directly, simply by asking for the
value of
\theta
which maximizes
\mathbb{P}(U|\theta).
It may also be derived by minimizing an empirical approximation of a KL divergence, an idea that we now explain and that we will use repeatedly in other places
in the book. Consider the minimization problem

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{F}}(\theta),

\displaystyle{\mathsf{F}}(\theta)

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\Upsilon\|q(\cdot\,;\theta)\bigr).

Since
\Upsilon
is unknown under Data Assumption
13.1
, we approximate the solution to this minimization problem by replacing the objective
{\mathsf{F}}(\cdot)
with an objective defined using only samples from
\Upsilon
.
Notice that the KL divergence defining the objective
{\mathsf{F}}

has the form

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\Upsilon\|q(\cdot\,;\theta)\bigr)=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon}\Bigl[\log\Upsilon(u)-\log q(u;\theta)\Bigr].

The first of the two terms does not depend on
\theta
, and hence minimizing
{\mathsf{F}}(\theta)
is the same as minimizing
the second term

-\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon}\Bigl[\log q(u;\theta)\Bigr]

over
\theta.
Replacing expectation with respect to
\Upsilon
with expectation with respect to the empirical measure
\Upsilon^{N}
defined in (
13.2
), we obtain

-\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon^{N}}\Bigl[\log q(u;\theta)\Bigr]=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,\log q(u^{(n)};\theta)=\frac{1}{N}{\mathsf{J}}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm{MLE}}}}^{N}(\theta).

Thus minimization of an empirical approximation of
{\mathsf{F}}(\theta)
is the same as computing the
minimizer of
{\mathsf{J}}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm{MLE}}}}^{N}(\cdot),
the MLE.

Remark 13.4
.

We have shown that finding the MLE is closely connected to finding the closest point in
\mathcal{Q}
to
\Upsilon
in the KL divergence.
Note, however, that the
constant term
\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon}\log\Upsilon(u)
, which we removed from the
optimization since it does not depend on
\theta
, is undefined if
\Upsilon
is replaced by
\Upsilon^{N}.

This issue arises elsewhere in this book, and in particular in the next subsection. We emphasize
the issue here, in the context of density estimation, since it constitutes the canonical setting in which it arises.
∎

13.2
Transport Methods

In this section we employ transport to characterize
\Upsilon\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, from the
samples defined by Data Assumption
13.1
. The methodology may be
viewed as a specific subclass of the MLE approach to density estimation described in the preceding
section. The subclass is one in which density
q
is defined via pushforward of a simple measure.
To be concrete, we specify a probability measure
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and determine
a map
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
with the property that (at least approximately)
\Upsilon=g_{\sharp}\zeta
.
The map
g
, then,
will be an approximate transport map (Definition
11.8
).
The desiderata are that
\zeta
be easy to sample, so that (approximate) samples from
\Upsilon
may be
generated by applying
g
to samples from
\zeta
;
that
g
may be determined
given only
\Upsilon^{N}
as defined in (
13.2
), and not
\Upsilon
itself; and that
g
be cheap to evaluate.

Imagine that a suitable choice for
\zeta
has been made; a Gaussian is a common
choice. Let
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
. We introduce a parametric family of
functions
g(\cdot\,;\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For our methodology it is
important that
g(\cdot\,;\theta)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
and is invertible, for all
\theta\in\Theta.

To this end we work with a function class
\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}
satisfying the condition that

(13.4)

\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}\subset\Bigl\{g(\cdot\,;\theta)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}),\,\forall\theta\in\Theta\,\Bigl|\det D_{u}g(u;\theta)>0\,\forall(u,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta\Bigr\}.

Such classes of smooth invertible functions

g(\cdot\,;\theta):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
will be discussed in Section
13.3
.

We then seek parameter
\theta\in\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that
g(\cdot\,;\theta):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
realizes the desired approximation.
To achieve this goal, we consider the optimization problem, over all
g
from
\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}
,

(13.5a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{F}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\Upsilon\|g(\cdot\,;\theta)_{\sharp}\zeta\bigr),

(13.5b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{F}}(\theta).

We define the resulting approximate pushforward
map by
g^{\star}=g(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star}).

As mentioned, this approach is a special case of the density estimation approach in
the preceding Section
13.1
: we parameterize the approximating densities
q(\cdot\,;\theta)=g(\cdot\,;\theta)_{\sharp}\zeta
as pushforward of a fixed reference density
\zeta
by a parameterized transport map
g(\cdot\,;\theta).

As in Section
13.1
, to optimize
{\mathsf{F}}(\cdot)

we will identify
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)
,
a function which differs from the KL divergence
only by a constant independent of
\theta,

and optimize
{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\cdot),
an empirical
approximation of
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)
found by replacing
\Upsilon
with
\Upsilon^{N}.

Lemma
11.45
is useful in determining
an explicit form of
{\mathsf{J}}
. We now deploy this lemma to
find a pair of expressions for
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)
.
From the definition of the KL divergence we have that

{\mathsf{F}}(\theta)=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\Upsilon\|g(\cdot\,;\theta)_{\sharp}\zeta\bigr)=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon}\Bigl[\log\Upsilon(u)-\log g_{\sharp}\zeta(u)\Bigr].

Thus, noting that the desired minimization is not affected by removing the first
\theta
-independent term, and using (
11.20
b) to simplify the second, we define

(13.6)

{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)=-\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon}\Bigl[\log\zeta\circ g^{-1}(u;\theta)+\log{\rm det}D_{u}(g^{-1})(u;\theta)\Bigr].

Our desired minimization problem for
{\mathsf{F}}(\cdot)

is thus equivalent to minimizing
(
13.6
). Furthermore, using (
11.19
) in (
13.6
), we may write

(13.7)

{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)=-\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon}\Bigl[\log\zeta\circ g^{-1}(u;\theta)-\log{\rm det}D_{u}g\bigl(g^{-1}(u;\theta);\theta\bigr)\Bigr].

In practice,
\Upsilon
is only available empirically and hence we work
under Data Assumption
13.1
. Hence, the objective
(
13.6
) or (
13.7
) can be approximated as follows:

(13.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta)&=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Bigl[\log\zeta\circ g^{-1}(u^{(n)};\theta)+\log{\rm det}D_{u}(g^{-1})(u^{(n)};\theta)\Bigr]\\
&=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Bigl[\log\zeta\circ g^{-1}(u^{(n)};\theta)-\log{\rm det}D_{u}g\bigl(g^{-1}(u^{(n)};\theta);\theta\bigr)\Bigr].\end{split}

Then the forward map
g^{\star}
is obtained at
\theta^{\star}
which minimizes
{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\cdot)
.

Remark 13.5
.

By minimizing (
13.8
) we thus find an approximate
expression for
\Upsilon
as the pushforward under
g^{\star}(\cdot)=g(\cdot,\theta^{\star})
of
\zeta
using (
11.20
). That is,

(13.9)

\Upsilon(u)\approx g^{\star}_{\sharp}\zeta(u)=(\zeta\circ g^{-1})(u;\theta^{\star}){\rm det}D_{u}g^{-1}(u;\theta^{\star}).

An important point to note here is that, to determine

g^{\star}
, minimization of either form of the loss function
{\mathsf{J}}^{N}
in (
13.8
)
requires evaluation of
g^{-1}.
Thus
g
must be readily invertible.
Section
13.3
addresses the issue of constructing invertible transports in the context
of normalizing flows, providing examples of classes of function
\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}.

∎

Remark 13.6
.

In this section
\zeta
is a simple measure, from which we can easily draw unlimited
samples – a unit Gaussian measure is a natural example; measure
\Upsilon
is a more complex measure only available to
us through
N
samples, and from which we wish to draw more samples. To this end we seek
g
such that

\Upsilon=g_{\sharp}\zeta
. Once the approximate transport
g^{\star}
is identified we can sample
\zeta
, apply
g^{\star}
to the sample,
and obtain new
(approximate) samples from
\Upsilon.

We may, however, use the same ideas in a different context. Assume now that

\Upsilon
is a known simple measure, from which samples are easily drawn, and
\zeta
is a more complicated measure
from which we wish to sample. Now the objective is to determine
T
so that
\zeta=T_{\sharp}\Upsilon.
We thus
consider the following variant on the minimization problem (
13.5
):

(13.10a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{F}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(T(\cdot\,;\theta)_{\sharp}\Upsilon\|\zeta\bigr),

(13.10b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{F}}(\theta).

By Theorem
11.46
this is equivalent, for invertible and sufficiently regular
T
, to

(13.11a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{F}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\Upsilon\|T^{-1}(\cdot\,;\theta)_{\sharp}\zeta\bigr),

(13.11b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{F}}(\theta).

and may thus be tackled by the same techniques described in this section, replacing
g^{-1}
by
T.

∎

Remark 13.7
.

Theorem
11.9
demonstrates that, through the Monge formulation of
optimal transport, there
exists
at least one perfect transport map
g_{\rm perfect}

for which
(g_{\rm perfect})_{\sharp}\zeta=\Upsilon
and hence
\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\Upsilon\|(g_{\rm perfect})_{\sharp}\zeta)=0
.
We aim to get as close as possible to
a
perfect transport solution, within our
parametric class, by minimizing
{\mathsf{F}}
over
\theta.
But we do not impose that we have
an optimal transport. The resulting variational methodologies resonate with the Monge
formulation of optimal transport, in that they are defined by a transport map, but we do not invoke the same optimality criterion.
∎

13.3
Normalizing Flows

The transport approach from Section
13.2
requires
invertibility of the map
g
, and efficient computation of the determinant
of the Jacobian. Invertibility of the map
g
is also useful in other
contexts. Normalizing flows address this issue;
normalizing

reflects the fact that distribution
\zeta
is often a Gaussian, whilst

flow
connotes the breaking up of the map
g
into a sequence of
simpler maps which are themselves parameterized, rather than
parameterizing
g
itself.
1
1

1

And, although we will derive a continuous-time analog of normalizing flows, the neural ODE, the use
of
flow

here should not be confused with its use in the theory
of continuous-time dynamical systems.
These simpler maps can be constructed to be readily
invertible, so that
g
is itself invertible; thus we address the concept of invertible transport
introduced in the previous section.

To this end we fix
J\in\mathbb{N}
and introduce the iteration

(13.12a)

\displaystyle v_{j+1}

\displaystyle=H(v_{j}\,;\theta),\quad j=0,\dots,J-1,

(13.12b)

\displaystyle v_{0}

\displaystyle=z,

where we define
u:=v_{J}
; here
H(\cdot\,;\theta):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Then

u=g(z\,;\theta)
where

(13.13)

g(\cdot\,;\theta)=\underbrace{H(\cdot\,;\theta)\circ H(\cdot\,;\theta)\circ\dots\circ H(\cdot\,;\theta)}_{J\text{ times}},

the
J
-fold
composition of
H(\cdot\,;\theta)
.
The proposed method is thus simply a transport of the type
discussed in Section
13.2
, with a specific
construction of
g
in compositional form. The remainder of
the section comprises two components: firstly showing
specifics of the formulation of the minimization problems
(
13.6
) or (
13.7
) in this compositional
setting; and secondly showing a continuum limit of the
compositional setting, which recovers neural ODEs.

Remark 13.8
.

We have concentrated on the simple setting where the same map
H
is used
at each step of the composition; this simplifies the exposition, especially in
the continuum limit. However it is commonplace to compose different
maps at each step, as discussed in Remark
13.9
; furthermore Remark
13.11

highlights the implications of doing so in the neural ODEs setting.
∎

13.3.1
Structure in the Optimization Problem for
\theta

We assume that, for all
\theta\in\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
H(\cdot\,;\theta):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

is invertible, with inverse

H^{-1}(\cdot\,;\theta):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, differentiable, with
derivative
DH(\cdot\,;\theta):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, and that
\det DH(\cdot,\theta)>0
everywhere
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Thus
g
given by (
13.13
) is an invertible transport.
Assume that
z\sim\zeta
and let
p_{j}(v_{j})

denote the density of
v_{j}
. Then the goal of a normalizing
flow is to choose parameter
\theta
in
H
so that

p_{J}\approx\Upsilon
and hence (approximately)

u\sim\Upsilon;
this is potentially a more
straightforward task than working with a directly parameterized
g,

the approach described in Section
13.2
.

Let
v_{j}=H^{(j)}(z;\theta)
, where
H^{(j)}(\cdot\,;\theta)
denotes the

j
-fold composition of
H(\cdot\,;\theta)
and recall that
u=g(z;\theta)=H^{(J)}(z;\theta)
.
Note, also, that
z
is found as the
J
-fold
composition of
H^{-1}(\cdot\,;\theta)
evaluated at
u
.
Formulae (
11.19
), (
11.20
) show that, since
v_{j}=H^{-1}(v_{j+1};\theta)
,

\log p_{j+1}(v_{j+1})=\log p_{j}(v_{j})-\log{\rm det}DH(v_{j};\theta).

By induction, we obtain that

\log p_{J}(v_{J})=\log p_{0}(v_{0})-\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\log{\rm det}DH(v_{j};\theta),

and hence

(13.14)

\log g_{\sharp}\zeta(u)=\log\zeta(z)-\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\log{\rm det}DH(v_{j};\theta).

In what follows we emphasize the dependence of
v_{j}
on
\theta,
writing
v_{j}(\theta).

Note that
{\mathsf{F}}(\theta)
given by (
13.5
) differs from

{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)=-\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon}\log g(\cdot\,;\theta)_{\sharp}\zeta

by a constant that is independent of
\theta
and that we minimize
an empirical approximation of
{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).
To this end, with the specified
relationships defined between
(z,\{v_{j}\})
and
u
,
we may consider the following specific case of (
13.7
),
using the normalization flow structure:

(13.15)

{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)=-\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon}\Bigl[\log\zeta(z)-\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\log{\rm det}DH\bigl(v_{j}(\theta);\theta\bigr)\Bigr].

In practice the expectation
over
\Upsilon
in (
13.15
) can be approximated using the empirical measure
\Upsilon^{N}
leading to
loss function
{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\cdot).

As before we set

\theta^{\star}\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta)

and define the resulting approximate pushforward map by

g^{\star}=g(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star}).

Note that
g(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})
is found from the
J
-fold
composition of
H(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})
.

Remark 13.9
.

The preceding setting can be generalized to one in which
each map in the composition is different and carries its
own set of parameters to be optimized, so that (
13.13
) is replaced by

(13.16)

g(\cdot\,;\theta)=H_{J-1}\bigl(\cdot\,;\theta^{(J-1)}\bigr)\circ H_{J-2}\bigl(\cdot\,;\theta^{(J-2)}\bigr)\circ\dots\circ H_{0}\bigl(\cdot\,;\theta^{(0)}\bigr),

and
\theta=\bigl\{\theta^{(j)}\bigr\}_{j=0}^{J-1}.

Then, for example, each
H_{j}
may have a triangular structure with respect to
some ordering of the elements of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Recall that the term triangular refers to the Jacobian of
H_{j}
being a (lower-) triangular matrix. One parameterization of a triangular map is given by

H_{j}(v;\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}[c]v_{1}\\
f_{2}(v_{1};\theta)+v_{2}\\
\vdots\\
f_{d}(v_{1},\dots,v_{d-1};\theta)+v_{d}\end{bmatrix},

where
f_{i}(v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1};\theta)
are parameterized functions.
Since triangular maps are readily
designed to be invertible, this approach can be useful; and choosing different
orderings at each step and hence different
H_{j}
at each step, mitigates
against bias from any particular ordering. While the illustrative parameteterization of a triangular map above is volume preserving, and thus potentially restrictive, more expressive parameterizations can be considered.
∎

13.3.2
Neural ODEs

Neural ODEs are a special case of transport maps
where map
g
is the solution map of an ordinary differential equation (ODE) with flow defined by a
parameterized vector field; finding
g
is replaced by the task of finding
the vector field. A continuum limit of normalizing
flows can be used to construct neural ODEs. To this end we define

(13.17)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{dv}{dt}&=h(v;\theta),\\
v(0)&=z.\end{split}

Noting that
v(t)
depends on
z
and on
\theta

we set
g_{t}(z;\theta)=v(t)
and
g(z;\theta)=g_{1}(z;\theta)=v(1).

An advantage of the continuum perspective is that the inverse
of map
g
is readily computed as follows. Define the equation

\displaystyle\frac{dw}{dt}

\displaystyle=-h(w;\theta),

\displaystyle w(0)

\displaystyle=u.

Then
g^{-1}_{t}(u;\theta)=w(t)
and, in particular,
g^{-1}(u;\theta)=w(1).

Thus, provided the regularity and structural properties of
h
are such as to ensure existence and uniqueness of solutions on the unit interval, both forward and backward, the map
g
defines an invertible transport.
We make this assumption in the remainder of this subsection.

In the following lemma we emphsize dependence of
v
on
\theta.

Lemma 13.10
.

Assume that in (
13.17
) the initial condition satisfies

z\sim\zeta
. Then

(13.18)

\log g_{\sharp}\zeta(u)=\log\zeta(z)-\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{div}\,h\bigl(v(s;\theta);\theta\bigr)\,ds.

Proof.

To establish this result, we work with the Euler approximation of the
continuum formulation, and pass to the limit of infinitesimally small
time-step. This may be achieved by considering the discrete
normalizing flow with

H(\cdot\,;\theta)=\mathrm{Id}+\Delta th(\cdot\,;\theta)
; setting
J\Delta t=1
,
where
\mathrm{Id}
is the identity map; then taking
\Delta t\to 0
will deliver the desired limit.
Note that the discrete and continuum pictures are connected by

v_{j}(\theta)\approx v(j\Delta t;\theta).
We do not provide a detailed
\epsilon-\delta

characterization of the limit
\Delta t\to 0
, instead proceeding purely formally.

We first note that

{\rm det}DH={\rm det}(I_{d}+\Delta tDh)=1+\Delta t{\rm Tr}Dh+{\mathcal{O}}(\Delta t^{2}).

Now note that
{\rm Tr}\,Dh=\mathrm{div}\,h.
Thus,
substituting this expression for
{\rm det}DH

into (
13.14
), expanding the logarithm in powers
of
\Delta t
, summing over
j
and letting
\Delta t\to 0
yields
the desired result.
∎

The continuum analog of (
13.15
) is then

(13.19)

{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)=-\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon}\Bigl[\log\zeta(z)-\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{div}\,h\bigl(v(s;\theta);\theta\bigr)\,ds\Bigr],

where
z=g^{-1}(u;\theta)
and
v(t;\theta)=g^{-1}_{1-t}(u;\theta).

As before, up to an additive constant,

{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)=\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\Upsilon\|g_{\sharp}\zeta).

Minimizing
{\mathsf{J}}
over
\theta
, to obtain
\theta^{\star}
,
thus leads to
g^{\star}(\cdot)=g(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star})
and

(g^{\star})_{\sharp}\zeta\approx\Upsilon.

Remark 13.11
.

Using the setting of Remark
13.9
leads
to a generalized form of neural ODE taking the form

(13.20)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{dv}{dt}&=h\bigl(v,t;\theta(t)\bigr),\\
v(0)&=z.\end{split}

Optimization is now over a function of time,
\theta(t)
, and
some form of regularization is needed to enforce continuity
of
\theta(\cdot)
. For example we may employ Tikhonov-Phillips
regularization,
penalizing the
H^{1}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{p})

norm of
\theta
, replacing
(
13.19
) by

(13.21)

{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)=-\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon}\Biggl[\log\zeta(z)-\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{div}\,h\bigl(v(s;\theta),s;\theta(s)\big)\,ds\Biggr]+\lambda\int_{0}^{1}\Bigl(|\theta(s)|^{2}+\bigl|\frac{d\theta}{dt}(s)\bigr|^{2}\Bigr)\,ds.

Notice that
z=v_{0}
and
v(s)
are functions of
u=v(1)
,
defined by the backward evolution of the ODE (
13.20
).
∎

13.4
Score-Based Approaches

So far in this chapter we have overviewed a variety of techniques for generative modeling,
all based around density estimation, with our focus being variants
on transport-based methods. An important shift of perspective, leading
to alternative methodologies, follows from understanding the role
of the Langevin equation
in generating samples from a measure with probability density
\Upsilon.

This is the subject of Subsection
13.4.1
. In the context of
Data Assumption
13.1
use of the Langevin equation
to generate approximate samples from
\Upsilon

requires learning the probability density function to be sampled,
or learning the
score function:
the gradient of the logarithm of the
probability density function. Subsections
13.4.2
and

13.4.3
study two approaches to learning the score. Throughout this section, we assume that
\Upsilon
is positive and sufficiently smooth for the expressions and integration-by-parts arguments below to be well defined.

13.4.1
Langevin Equation

We start by defining the Langevin equation

(13.22)

du(t)=D\log\Upsilon\bigl(u(t)\bigr)dt+\sqrt{2}dW(t),\quad u(0)\sim\zeta.

Under certain tail and smoothness assumptions on
\Upsilon
this equation
is ergodic: for suitable classes of test function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R},

we have that, almost surely with respect to
u(0)
and
W(\cdot)
(assumed
independent of one another),

\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\varphi\bigl(u(t)\bigr)\,dt=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon}\bigl[\varphi(u)\bigr].

Similar convergence results hold in law.
This statement of ergodicity may be viewed as defining a stochastic transport,
over an infinite time horizon, of
\zeta
into
\Upsilon;
stochastic because it depends on
Brownian motion
W(\cdot)
and infinite time horizon because
u(0)\sim\zeta

whilst
u(T)\sim\Upsilon
in the limit
T\rightarrow\infty
. Note that, because of ergodicity,
wide classes of
\zeta
all get transported to the desired measure
\Upsilon.

We wish to use simulation of the Langevin equation as the basis for drawing approximate samples
from
\Upsilon.
However, we do not assume that we are given
\Upsilon
; we simply have
the data
U
from Data Assumption
13.1
. Thus we must learn
the
score

D\log\Upsilon

from data
U.
One approach to deal with this issue is to employ density estimation from Section
13.1
to approximate
\Upsilon
and then, provided the approximation is sufficiently smooth, use the Langevin approach to sample.
But since only the gradient of the logarithm of
\Upsilon
enters the Langevin equation, we can instead try to directly learn this function from data. Doing so is the topic of the next two subsections.

Remark 13.12
.

Once the score is known, then discretization of the Langevin equation in
time, for example by the Euler–Maruyama method, and integration to a large time,
provides a way of approximating samples from
\Upsilon.
However, discretization
may cause ergodicity to be lost. One approach to overcome this is to view the
discretization as a proposal distribution for the
Metropolis–Hastings MCMC
methods introduced in Section
15.6
.
∎

13.4.2
Score-Matching

The starting point is to identify a function
s_{\theta}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
so that
s_{\theta^{\star}}\approx D\log\Upsilon.
We assume that
\theta
is chosen from set
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
. We work under
Data Assumption
13.1
and hence are given only samples from
\Upsilon
: we do not assume access to the target density
\Upsilon
itself.
A natural objective results in the following regression problem:

(13.23a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{F}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|s_{\theta}(u)-D\log\Upsilon(u)|^{2}\Upsilon(u)\,du,

(13.23b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{F}}(\theta).

Then
s^{\star}:=s_{\theta^{\star}}
is the approximate score at the optimal
\theta.

This approach looks attractive, but approximate evaluation
of the objective
{\mathsf{F}}(\cdot)
, using only knowledge of the empirical
measure (
13.2
), and directly employing
(
13.23
), is not feasible in practice.
The root cause is the presence of the true score
D\log\Upsilon(u)

in the objective. The reader will note that
\log\Upsilon(u)
also entered the
objective
{\mathsf{F}}
arising in density estimation (Section
13.1
).
However in that context the terms involving
\log\Upsilon(u)
additively decouple
from those involving the unknown parameter
\theta.
The same happens here, but an integration
by parts is necessary to make this apparent.

Proposition 13.13
.

Assume that
K:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D\log\Upsilon(u)|^{2}\Upsilon(u)\,du<\infty
and that, for every
\theta\in\Theta
, the integration-by-parts identity

(13.24)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle s_{\theta}(u),D\Upsilon(u)\rangle\,du=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{div}\,s_{\theta}(u)\Upsilon(u)\,du

holds. Then

\displaystyle{\mathsf{F}}(\theta)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{J}}(\theta)+K,

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Bigl(|s_{\theta}(u)|^{2}+2\,\mathrm{div}\,s_{\theta}(u)\Bigr)\Upsilon(u)\,du.

Thus
\theta^{\star}
defined by (
13.23
) also satisfies

(13.26)

\theta^{\star}\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).

Proof.

Expanding the Euclidean norm, the least-squares objective (
13.23
) yields

\displaystyle{\mathsf{F}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Bigl(|s_{\theta}(u)|^{2}-2\bigl\langle s_{\theta}(u),D\log\Upsilon(u)\bigr\rangle+|D\log\Upsilon(u)|^{2}\Bigr)\Upsilon(u)\,du.

Using

D\log\Upsilon(u)\Upsilon(u)=D\Upsilon(u)

and the integration-by-parts identity
(
13.24
), we obtain

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigl\langle s_{\theta}(u),D\log\Upsilon(u)\bigr\rangle\Upsilon(u)\,du=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigl\langle s_{\theta}(u),D\Upsilon(u)\bigr\rangle\,du=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\mathrm{div}\,s_{\theta}(u)\Upsilon(u)\,du.

The desired result follows.
∎

Objective function
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)
is readily approximated under Data Assumption
13.1
, leading to

{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Bigl(|s_{\theta}(u)|^{2}+2\,\mathrm{div}\,s_{\theta}(u)\Bigr)\Upsilon^{N}(u)\,du.

Actionable algorithms to approximate the score may then proceed by optimizing
{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\cdot).

Remark 13.14
.

Let
\Upsilon_{\theta}
be a distribution whose score is given by
s_{\theta}=D\log\Upsilon_{\theta}
. Then, the objective
{\mathsf{F}}(\cdot)
corresponds to the squared

Fisher divergence

d_{F}(\Upsilon\|\Upsilon_{\theta})^{2}\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D\log\Upsilon_{\theta}(u)-D\log\Upsilon(u)|^{2}\Upsilon(u)\,du.

∎

Remark 13.15
.

We note that, in contrast to (
13.23
), the objective
(
13.26
) requires evaluation of
the gradient of the approximate score function
Ds_{\theta}
, in order to evaluate the divergence term. If
s_{\theta}
is parameterized as the gradient of a scalar function, then this
gradient is a Hessian matrix of size
d\times d
and can be challenging to compute and store in high dimensions. However, one may leverage techniques from randomized linear algebra to estimate the trace of this matrix.
The need to evaluate the Hessian can also be avoided by learning the score
not of
\Upsilon
but of its convolution with a Gaussian.
∎

13.4.3
Denoising Score-Matching

To avoid the need to evaluate the Hessian we introduce the idea of

denoising score-matching
.
This approach estimates the score function
for an approximate distribution defined by the convolution of
\Upsilon

with a kernel
p_{\sigma}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}

with bandwidth
\sigma>0
. That is,

(13.27)

\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{\sigma}(u,w)\Upsilon(w)\,dw.

For example,
p_{\sigma}
can be a Gaussian kernel
(recall the Definition
11.19
of kernel)

(13.28)

p_{\sigma}(u,w)\propto\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}|u-w|^{2}\Bigr).

This choice is motivated by the facts that: (i) for bandwidth

\sigma\rightarrow 0
we obtain that
p_{\sigma}(\cdot\,;w)
converges
to the Dirac measure
\delta_{w}
, if appropriately normalized,
and so
\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)
converges to
\Upsilon(u)
, the original
distribution; (ii) on the other hand, larger bandwidths smooth
larger-scale features of
\Upsilon
. The following proposition shows
that score-matching for
\Upsilon_{\sigma}

can be performed without knowing the score
of
\Upsilon
directly. To this end we define

(13.29a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{F}}_{\sigma}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|s_{\theta}(u)-D\log\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)|^{2}\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)\,du,

(13.29b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{F}}_{\sigma}(\theta).

Proposition 13.16
.

Assume that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D\log\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)|^{2}\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)\,du+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D_{u}\log p_{\sigma}(u,w)|^{2}p_{\sigma}(u,w)\Upsilon({w})\,dw\,du<\infty.

Then
\theta
solving (
13.29
) also solves the minimization problem

(13.30)

\theta^{\star}\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|s_{\theta}(u)-D_{u}\log p_{\sigma}(u,w)|^{2}p_{\sigma}(u,w)\Upsilon(w)\,du\,dw.

Proof.

The score of the smoothed distribution
\Upsilon_{\sigma}
is given by

\displaystyle D\log\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)=\frac{D\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)}{\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{u}p_{\sigma}(u,w)\Upsilon(w)\,dw

(13.31)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{u}\bigl(\log p_{\sigma}(u,w)\bigr)p_{\sigma}(u,w)\Upsilon(w)\,dw.

Thus, the inner product of
s_{\theta}
and the score of the smoothed distribution is given by

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigl\langle s_{\theta}(u),D\log\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)\bigr\rangle\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)\,du=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigl\langle s_{\theta}(u),D_{u}\log p_{\sigma}(u,w)\bigr\rangle p_{\sigma}(u,w)\Upsilon(w)\,dw\,du.

Furthermore

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|s_{\theta}(u)|^{2}\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)\,du=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|s_{\theta}(u)|^{2}p_{\sigma}(u,w)\Upsilon(w)\,dw\,du

by definition of
\Upsilon_{\sigma}.

Substituting the two preceding expressions into the objective
{\mathsf{F}}_{\sigma}(\theta)

gives

\displaystyle{\mathsf{F}}_{\sigma}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|s_{\theta}(u)-D_{u}\log p_{\sigma}(u,w)|^{2}p_{\sigma}(u,w)\Upsilon(w)\,du\,dw+K,

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D\log\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)|^{2}\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)\,du-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D_{u}\log p_{\sigma}(u,w)|^{2}p_{\sigma}(u,w)\Upsilon(w)\,du\,dw.

Noting that
K
is independent of
\theta
and finite by assumption, we deduce that the minimizers of
{\mathsf{F}}_{\sigma}(\cdot)
coincide with those defined in (
13.30
).
∎

We now restrict our attention to the specific case where
p_{\sigma}(u,w)

is the conditional density of
u
given
w

defined by (
13.28
).
The score of smoothed distribution
\Upsilon_{\sigma}
that is computed by Proposition
13.16
can then
be expressed in terms of a conditional expectation of the underlying random variables. The next lemma presents this result, which is known as
Tweedie’s formula
.

Lemma 13.17
.

Let
u=w+\sigma\eta
where
w\sim\Upsilon
and
\eta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})

and let
p_{\sigma}(\cdot\,;w)
denote the Gaussian probability density function
defined by kernel(
13.28
).
Define
\pi^{w}
to be the posterior density
2
2

2

Note that this is the correct normalization
by (
13.27
).

\pi^{w}(w|u)=\frac{1}{\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)}p_{\sigma}(u,w)\Upsilon(w).

Then the score
D\log\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)
satisfies the identity

(13.32)

\mathbb{E}^{w\sim\pi^{w}}[w]=u+\sigma^{2}D\log\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u).

Proof.

First note that the score of the Gaussian probability density function is

D_{u}\log p_{\sigma}(u,w)=-\frac{(u-w)}{\sigma^{2}}.

Using this identity in (
13.31
)
we can write the score of the smoothed distribution as

\displaystyle D\log\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{u}\bigl(\log p_{\sigma}(u,w)\bigr)p_{\sigma}(u,w)\Upsilon(w)\,dw

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{-(u-w)}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{p_{\sigma}(u,w)\Upsilon(w)}{\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)}\,dw

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\mathbb{E}^{w\sim\pi^{w}}[w-u],

and the desired result follows.
∎

Tweedie’s formula shows that computing a score function is related to an inverse problem. It may be rearranged
to give:

D\log\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\Bigl(\mathbb{E}^{w\sim\pi^{w}}[w]-u\Bigr).

Given a noisy realization
u
of the random variable
w,
the score is given in terms of the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}[w|u]
, which is the posterior mean for the unknown
w
. Notice that
\mathbb{E}[w|u]
is the function of
u
that minimizes
\mathbb{E}|w-\varphi(u)|^{2}
over measurable maps
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Thus, to learn
D\log\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)
we can seek parameterized map
\varphi_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
that minimizes
\mathbb{E}|w-\varphi_{\theta}(u)|^{2}.
This problem can be empirically approximated without having access to the density
\Upsilon
. Specifically, given only data
\{w^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
independently sampled from
\Upsilon,
we set
u^{(n)}:=w^{(n)}+\sigma\eta^{(n)},
where
\eta^{(n)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
i.i.d. We then set
\theta^{\star}\in\argmin_{\theta}\sum_{n=1}^{N}|w^{(n)}-\varphi_{\theta}(u^{(n)})|^{2},
define
\varphi^{\star}=\varphi_{\theta^{\star}}
and approximate

D\log\Upsilon_{\sigma}(u)\approx\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bigl(\varphi^{\star}(u)-u\bigr)
.

13.5
Autoencoders

Autoencoders are primarily used as a technique for uncovering
latent low-dimensional structure in high-dimensional data. We introduce
them here and then demonstrate in the next section that a natural
probabilistic relaxation of the idea leads to generative models
termed variational autoencoders.

Transport ideas may be used to (approximately) represent a complicated
probability measure, perhaps only known through samples, as the
pushforward of a simpler measure. In this section
we go a step further, by seeking the simpler measure in a
latent space of lower dimension than
that of the ambient space where the data lives.
Autoencoders are one natural approach to such problems.

The basic idea of autoencoders is to find an approximate factorization
of the identity which is accurate on the support of density
\Upsilon
; furthermore we
need to do this using only the empirical approximation of
\Upsilon
by
\Upsilon^{N}
.
Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta_{f}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}

and
g:\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}\times\Theta_{g}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
where
\Theta_{f}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p_{f}}
and
\Theta_{g}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p_{g}}.
Function
f
is referred to as the
encoder
and
g
as the
decoder
. Recalling that
\mathrm{Id}
denotes the
identity mapping on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, define
3
3

3

The notation

H_{\Upsilon}
and
H_{\Upsilon}^{N}
was introduced in the previous chapter in the
context of real-valued functions; see equation (
12.2
) and the material following it.
This notation readily generalizes to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued functions
.

{\mathsf{J}}(\theta_{f},\theta_{g})=\Bigl\|\mathrm{Id}-g\bigl(f(\cdot\,;\theta_{f});\theta_{g}\bigr)\Bigr\|_{H_{\Upsilon}}^{2}:=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon}\bigl|u-g\bigl(f(u;\theta_{f});\theta_{g}\bigr)\bigr|^{2}.

Now consider the following optimization problem:

(13.33)

\displaystyle(\theta^{\star}_{f},\theta^{\star}_{g})\in\argmin_{(\theta_{f},\theta_{g})\in\Theta_{f}\times\Theta_{g}}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta_{f},\theta_{g}).

We then define
f^{\star}(u)=f(u;\theta^{\star}_{f})
and
g^{\star}(z)=g(z;\theta^{\star}_{g}).

Roughly speaking, and dropping the dependence on parameters for
expository purposes, we are seeking functions
f
and
g

such that
g\bigl(f(u)\bigr)\approx u.
In practice the
optimization is implemented empirically, and we minimize

{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta_{f},\theta_{g})=\Bigl\|\mathrm{Id}-g\bigl(f(\cdot\,;\theta_{f});\theta_{g}\bigr)\Bigr\|_{H_{\Upsilon}^{N}}^{2}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bigl|u^{(n)}-g\bigl(f(u^{(n)};\theta_{f});\theta_{g}\bigr)\bigr|^{2}.

Remark 13.18
.

This approach reduces the autoencoder to a particular form of supervised learning in which the inputs and outputs are equal, so we seek to
approximate the identity as composition of
g
with
f
:
g\circ f\approx\mathrm{Id}.

We impose a specific structure on the class of approximating functions; for example each of
f
and
g
can be a neural network,
as defined in Section
12.2
, generalized to vector-valued output.
We refer to
\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}
as the
latent space

and note that a primary application of the methodology
is to identify latent spaces of dimension

d_{z}
which is much less than the dimension
d
of the data space. The
approximate factorization of the identity found by composing
g
with
f

provides a way of moving between the data space and the latent space.
∎

Example 13.19
.

We demonstrate that
principal component analysis
,
often referred to simply as PCA, may be viewed
as a form of autoencoder.
After centering
the data, we may assume that

\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon}u=0.

Define the covariance matrix

C=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon}[u\otimes u].

Then consider the eigenpairs
(\varphi^{(i)},\lambda^{(i)})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
solving

\displaystyle C\varphi^{(i)}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{(i)}\varphi^{(i)},

\displaystyle|\varphi^{(i)}|

\displaystyle=1.

The eigenvalues are non-negative and we assume them to be decreasingly ordered
with respect to
i.
Moreover, the eigenvectors can be chosen to be orthonormal.

For given
d_{z}\leq d,
we now define the maps

\displaystyle f(u)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\langle u,\varphi^{(1)}\rangle,\ldots,\langle u,\varphi^{(d_{z})}\rangle\Bigr),

\displaystyle g(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d_{z}}z_{j}\varphi^{(j)}.

Then, their composition gives us

\displaystyle g\bigl(f(u)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d_{z}}\langle u,\varphi^{(j)}\rangle\varphi^{(j)}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\sum_{j=1}^{d_{z}}\varphi^{(j)}\otimes\varphi^{(j)}\Bigr)u.

Thus, the PCA method may be viewed as truncating the representation
of the identity defined by the
spectral theorem for positive,
symmetric matrices. The specific basis used is defined by

\Upsilon
but it is approximated in practice knowing only
\Upsilon^{N}
, after centering the data by their
empirical mean and computing their empirical covariance.
∎

13.6
Variational Autoencoders

In the preceding section, note that
\zeta=(f^{\star})_{\sharp}\Upsilon

gives the (approximate) distribution
in the latent space, but that we do not specify
\zeta
.
Furthermore the mapping

g
(approximately) solves a measure transport problem from
\zeta
to
\Upsilon
,
but the choice of measure
\zeta
is not within our control.
It is natural to ask whether the methodology can be
generalized to settings in which it is desirable
to impose a specified distribution
\zeta
. This leads to the topic
of
variational autoencoders
,
which we now outline.

Let
\pi
be a coupling of
\Upsilon
and
\zeta

and note that the following two identities hold:

\displaystyle\pi(u,z)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(u|z)\zeta(z),

\displaystyle\pi(u,z)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(z|u)\Upsilon(u).

The idea of the variational autoencoder
is to approximate the two conditional densities
\mathbb{P}(u|z),\mathbb{P}(z|u)
,
appearing in these identities, by parameterized families; and then
to align the two different expressions for the coupling. To be explicit
we assume that

\displaystyle u|z

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}\bigl(g(z;\theta_{g}),\sigma_{g}^{2}I\bigr),

\displaystyle z|u

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}\bigl(f(u;\theta_{f}),\sigma_{f}^{2}I\bigr).

Here the domains and ranges of
f,g
are as specified in Section
13.5
.
The assumptions on the conditionals
u|z
and
z|u
thus constitute a relaxation of the setting
for autoencoders.
These two identities yield
\mathbb{P}(u|z;\theta_{g})\approx\mathbb{P}(u|z)
and

\mathbb{P}(z|u;\theta_{f})\approx\mathbb{P}(z|u).

Then parameter choices to define
f
and
g
are made to ensure that the two
resulting approximate expressions for
\pi(u,z)
are close.
Invoking these Gaussian approximations for the conditionals we obtain
the following two approximations for the coupling density:

\displaystyle\pi_{g}(u,z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{g}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2\sigma_{g}^{2}}|u-g(z;\theta_{g})|^{2}\Bigr)\zeta(z),

\displaystyle\pi_{f}(u,z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{f}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2\sigma_{f}^{2}}|z-f(u;\theta_{f})|^{2}\Bigr)\Upsilon(u).

A common choice is to assume that
\zeta
is the density of
a standard unit Gaussian; we make this assumption throughout what follows. We will only discuss learning of the parameters defining
(f,g)
,
but
(\sigma_{g},\sigma_{f})
could also be learned.

We ask that
\pi_{f}
and
\pi_{g}
are close in
KL divergence,
with
\pi_{f}
in the first argument.
Then, using the Gaussian assumptions
on the conditionals and on
\zeta
,
leads to insightful explicit calculations.
To see this, we first note that

(13.34)

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\pi_{f}\|\pi_{g})=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon}\biggl[\mathbb{E}^{z\sim\mathbb{P}(z|u;\theta_{f})}\Bigl[\frac{1}{2\sigma_{g}^{2}}|u-g(z;\theta_{g})|^{2}\Bigr]+\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(\mathbb{P}(\cdot|u;\theta_{f})\|\zeta\bigr)\biggr]+{\mathsf{const}},

where the constant is independent of the parameters we wish to learn
and where
\mathbb{P}(z|u)
and
\zeta(z)
are given by their assumed Gaussian structure.
Using the expression in Example
11.40
for
the KL
divergence between two Gaussians, and dropping the terms that
depend only on the (assumed known) variances, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\pi_{f}\|\pi_{g})=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon}\biggl[\mathbb{E}^{z\sim\mathcal{N}\bigl(f(u;\theta_{f}),\sigma_{f}^{2}I\bigr)}\Bigl[\frac{1}{2\sigma_{g}^{2}}\big|u-g(z;\theta_{g})\big|^{2}\Bigr]+\frac{1}{2}|f(u;\theta_{f})|^{2}\biggr]+{\mathsf{const}}.

We may now use the
reparameterization trick

(see Subsection
15.7.3
)
to rewrite the desired objective function.
Noting that
z\sim\mathcal{N}\bigl(f(u;\theta_{f}),\sigma_{f}^{2}I\bigr)
is
the same in law as
f(u;\theta_{f})+\sigma_{f}\xi
, where
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I),
we may write the desired divergence as

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(\pi_{f}\|\pi_{g})={\mathsf{J}}(\theta_{f},\theta_{g})+\mathsf{const},

where

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta_{f},\theta_{g})=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon}\biggl[\mathbb{E}^{\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)}\Bigl[\frac{1}{2\sigma_{g}^{2}}\Big|u-g\bigl(f(u;\theta_{f})+\sigma_{f}\xi;\theta_{g}\big)\Big|^{2}\Bigr]+\frac{1}{2}|f(u;\theta_{f})|^{2}\biggr]

and the constant term is independent of
(\theta_{f},\theta_{g}).

This constitutes a regularized version of the standard autoencoder;
in particular the preceding expression for the divergence clearly
regularizes the basic concept that
g\bigl(f(u)\bigr)\approx u

under density
\Upsilon.

Remark 13.20
.

In practice this is implemented with expectation
over
\Upsilon
approximated empirically by
\Upsilon^{N}.

This leaves an optimization problem in which the objective
is defined via expectation over
\xi.
Stochastic
gradient descent from
Chapter
15

may be used to tackle this problem. After training, approximate samples
from
\Upsilon
may be obtained by sampling
z\sim\zeta
and then
sampling
u|z\sim\mathcal{N}\bigl(g(z;\theta^{\star}_{g}),\sigma_{g}^{2}I\big)
.

Notice that in (
13.34
) we employ the expectation

\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon}\mathbb{E}^{z\sim\mathbb{P}(z|u;\theta_{f})}.

Using empiricalization this is approximated by

\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon^{N}}\mathbb{E}^{z\sim\mathbb{P}(z|u;\theta_{f})}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}^{z\sim\mathbb{P}(z|u^{(n)};\theta_{f})},

where the
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
are the i.i.d. samples
from
\Upsilon
that comprise
\Upsilon^{N}.

The resulting expression involves the
N
probability measures
\mathbb{P}(z|u^{(n)};\theta_{f})
. These are
referred to as
N
posterior distributions in the original
literature on this subject as they are (approximate) posteriors on the latent
z
, one for each data point
u^{(n)}.

∎

13.7
Generative Adversarial Networks

The variational autoencoder trains
a probabilistic model for
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by learning
\mathbb{P}(u|z)

and specifying
\mathbb{P}(z).

The generative adversarial network is
another method for training such a probabilistic model, which
also allows for low-dimensional latent space.
To be concrete we assume that it has the same structural
form, namely

(13.35a)

\displaystyle u|z

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}\bigl(g(z;\theta_{g}),\sigma_{g}^{2}I\bigr),

(13.35b)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}\bigl(0,I\bigr).

Once again the domain and range of

g
are as specified in Section
13.5
.

With the probabilistic construction (
13.35
),
samples from
u
are created from samples from
z
. We
write the resulting density for
u
, under this generative model,
as
\Upsilon_{g}
; here
g
connotes the fact that the model is generative.
It is desired that samples from
\Upsilon_{g}
are similar to those
specified by the data, which is drawn from density
\Upsilon
.
The generative adversarial network starts by defining a

discriminator

\mathsf{d}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta_{\mathsf{d}}\to[0,1]
,
to be thought of as taking values which are probabilities.
Here
\Theta_{\mathsf{d}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{p_{\mathsf{d}}}.

It is instructive to think of
\mathsf{d}(u;\theta_{\mathsf{d}})
as being the
probability that
u
is drawn from the density
\Upsilon
, and

1-\mathsf{d}(u;\theta_{\mathsf{d}})
as the probability of
u
being drawn
from the generative model
\Upsilon_{g}.

We then define
v:\Theta_{g}\times\Theta_{\mathsf{d}}\to\mathbb{R}
by

v(\theta_{g},\theta_{\mathsf{d}})=\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon}\Bigl[\log\mathsf{d}(u;\theta_{\mathsf{d}})\Bigr]+\mathbb{E}^{u\sim\Upsilon_{g}}\Bigl[\log\bigl(1-\mathsf{d}(u;\theta_{\mathsf{d}})\bigr)\Bigr].

The optimal parameter values are defined by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\theta}_{\mathsf{d}}(\theta_{g})

\displaystyle\in\argmax_{\theta_{\mathsf{d}}\in\Theta_{\mathsf{d}}}v(\theta_{g},\theta_{\mathsf{d}}),

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}_{g}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta_{g}\in\Theta_{g}}v\bigl(\theta_{g},\widetilde{\theta}_{\mathsf{d}}(\theta_{g})\bigr),

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}_{\mathsf{d}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\theta}_{\mathsf{d}}(\theta^{\star}_{g}).

In the maximization step, the parameters of the discriminator are chosen
to maximize
v
– the discriminator acts adversarially to try and find data
under
\Upsilon_{g}
which does not look like data under
\Upsilon
. In the minimization
step the generator acts to reduce
v
to try and make the two data sources
look similar. Ideally, through an iterative process, a solution is found in
which
\mathsf{d}(\cdot\,;\theta^{\star}_{\mathsf{d}})\equiv\frac{1}{2}
, so that the data and generated
data are indistinguishable. The value of
\theta^{\star}_{g}
defines the generative
model once this indistinguishable state has been reached.

the book
[

]
and the literature cited there. For a foundational reference concerning the use of normalizing
flows
in machine learning, see
[

]
; more recent
applications may be found in
[

]
,
[

]
.
The neural ODE perspective was popularized in the paper

[

; earlier papers
[

]
anticipated the idea but did not develop it into a practical tool in the same way as

]
.

and systematically
developed in

]
; computational
methodology is overviewed and developed in

[

]
.
Triangular transport maps are overviewed as a method for sampling in

[

]
.

Score-based methods have become popular in the context of generative diffusion models
[

]
.
The integration by parts method and denoising score-matching approaches were first proposed in
[

]
and
[

]
, respectively. These approximate scores have been used for sampling using Langevin dynamics; see
[

,

]
for sampling guarantees. They have also been used in other unsupervised learning tasks, like learning probabilistic graphical models. Autoencoders
have a long history; see

]

and the citations therein.

]
.

]
.

in

developed in

,

]

in the solution of inverse problems, is undertaken in

,

,

,

,

,

]
.

Chapter 14
Time-Series Forecasting

This chapter is devoted to the study of time-series forecasting,
with an emphasis on data-driven approaches.
In most other chapters we have used
n
to index data and
j
to
index (discrete) time in (possibly stochastic) dynamical systems.
In this chapter the two are conflated as the data comes from
a time series as follows:

Data Assumption 14.1
.

Let
\mathsf{N}=\{0,1,\ldots,N\}.

We are given a time series
V^{\dagger}=\{v^{\dagger}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathsf{N}}
,
of elements
v^{\dagger}_{n}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}.

For this reason the (possibly stochastic) dynamical
systems in this
chapter will be indexed by
n
, not
j.

Our focus in this chapter is to learn models that are consistent
with the data in Data Assumption
14.1
and which may be
used to predict future outcomes from points not in the data set.

Section
14.1
contains background on ergodic
and stationary processes.
In Sections
14.2
and
14.3

we study deterministic models to fit the time-series data,
and in Section
14.4
we study random models.
Section
14.2
is devoted to the study of analog forecasting methods;
these methods seek to predict
from a given point by looking at what happened, in the data set,
starting at nearby point(s).
In Section
14.3
we look at fitting Markovian deterministic
models to the data; we also study non-Markovian deterministic models,
using a recurrent structure to capture memory dependence.
In Section
14.4
we introduce
autoregressive models; we study linear autoregressive models,
modelling the data as a Gaussian process,
and non-Gaussian autoregressive models, using transport ideas.
We conclude in Section
14.5
with bibliographic remarks.

14.1
Ergodic and Stationary Processes

Suppose that
\{v^{\dagger}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
is generated by the map
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the iteration, assumed to hold for
all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,

(14.1)

v^{\dagger}_{n+1}=\psi(v^{\dagger}_{n});

complete specification of
\{v^{\dagger}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
then follows once
v^{\dagger}_{0}
is given.
We extend
\psi
to act on Borel subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and denote by
\psi^{(n)}
its
n
-fold
composition. We assume that there exists a set
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
which
supports an invariant measure
\mu
; that is, a measure such that if
v\sim\mu
then
\psi(v)\sim\mu.

In particular, if
v^{\dagger}_{0}\sim\mu
then
v^{\dagger}_{n}\sim\mu
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}.

In terms of pushforward we have
\psi_{\sharp}\mu=\mu.

Example 14.2
.

Assume that
\psi
is continuous and has the property that there is a compact
absorbing
set

\mathcal{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
: for any compact subset
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
there is

M=M(B)
such that
\psi^{(m)}(B)\subseteq\mathcal{B}
for all
m\geq M
.
Then
\psi
has a
global attractor
: a unique compact invariant set
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
that attracts all bounded sets
B
: the Hausdorff distance
h(\psi^{(m)}(B),\mathcal{A})\to 0
as
m\to\infty.

The global attractor
\mathcal{A}
is defined by

\mathcal{A}:=\bigcap_{M\geq 0}\overline{\bigcup_{m\geq M}\psi^{(m)}(\mathcal{B})}.

If
\psi(\mathcal{B})\subseteq\mathcal{B}
then
the global attractor may be characterized more directly by the formula

\mathcal{A}=\bigcap_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\psi^{(m)}(\mathcal{B}).

The global attractor
\mathcal{A}
supports an invariant measure
\mu.

∎

We employ the following informal definition of an ergodic dynamical system.
The process
\{v^{\dagger}_{n}\}
, in
\mathbb{R}^{d},
is

ergodic
with respect to invariant measure
\mu
if,
for a sufficiently wide class of test functions
\varphi
,

(14.2)

\lim_{M\to\infty}\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}\varphi(v^{\dagger}_{m})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(v)\,\mu(dv),

with the convergence being almost sure with respect to
v^{\dagger}_{0}
drawn
from
\mu.

Since the process is deterministic, it is possible that measure

\mu
will not have density with respect to Lebesgue measure.
This is why, in contrast to much of the remainder of the notes,
we have not used a probability density function in this
definition.

Remark 14.3
.

Ergodicity for a
stochastic
rather than deterministic Markov process
\{v^{\dagger}_{n}\}
can be defined similarly by requiring that there exists measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that, for a sufficiently wide class of test functions
\varphi,
equation (
14.2
) holds; the almost sure property is then with respect to both the initial condition and
the noise process driving the stochastic evolution. For both deterministic and stochastic settings, (
14.2
) encapsulates the idea that averaging over time is equivalent to averaging over space with respect to
\mu
: the expected value of
\varphi
with respect to measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be computed by averaging the values of
\varphi
along a trajectory of the process. This idea also underpins
Markov chain Monte Carlo algorithms; this methodology is
described in Section
15.6
.
∎

The following definition applies to both deterministic dynamical systems, randomly initialized, and to
random dynamical systems:

Definition 14.4
.

A discrete-time dynamical system
giving rise to data
\{v^{\dagger}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
is said to be

strictly stationary
if,
for all
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, the joint distribution of

(v^{\dagger}_{n},\ldots,v^{\dagger}_{n+p})
is the same for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.
It is said to be
wide-sense stationary
if, for all
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, the first and second moments of

(v^{\dagger}_{n},\ldots,v^{\dagger}_{n+p})
are the same for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.
∎

For stationary processes it is natural to
make the following definition.

Definition 14.5
.

For given
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, the
correlation measure

\pi_{m}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
of a stationary process
\{v^{\dagger}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
is the distribution of the pair
(v^{\dagger}_{n},v^{\dagger}_{n+m}).
The
two-point correlation function

of a wide-sense stationary process
\{v^{\dagger}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
is function
c:\mathbb{Z}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
given by
c(m):=\mathbb{E}[v^{\dagger}_{n}(v^{\dagger}_{n+m})^{\top}].

∎

Remark 14.6
.

Note that the two-point correlation function is sometimes defined as the scalar-valued
function found as the trace of
c(\cdot).
The matrix-valued definition we adopt here is, however, more widely useful
and implies the scalar-valued definition by applying the particular linear functional defined by the trace operation.
∎

Example 14.7
.

In the deterministic setting of
(
14.1
) randomness can only enter through the initial condition.
If
v_{0}\sim\mu
then the correlation measure
\pi_{m}(\cdot,\cdot)
is simply the
pushforward of
\mu
under the map
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\bigl(u,\psi^{(m)}(u)\bigr)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}.

∎

14.1.1
Lyapunov Exponents

Under conditions described by Oseledet’s Theorem (see Bibliography
Section
14.5
), closely related to the preceding informal definition
of ergodicity, we can define the maximal

Lyapunov exponent

of
\psi
as

(14.3)

\lambda_{\text{max}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{n}\log\frac{|\psi^{(n)}(v^{\dagger}+\epsilon e)-\psi^{(n)}(v^{\dagger})|}{\epsilon},

for
\mu
-almost every
v^{\dagger}
and almost every vector
e
picked uniformly at random from the unit sphere. For
chaotic
systems
the maximal exponent will be larger than zero.

For expository purposes consider the case
\psi(v)=Av
where
A
is symmetric and positive definite, and suppose that its largest eigenvalue
\gamma
is simple, with corresponding unit eigenvector
\varphi
.
This does not satisfy the standard assumptions of the theory, but is simple
enough to provide intuition. Provided that
the projection of
e
onto
\varphi
is non-zero (which will happen almost surely)
it is clear that
\lambda_{\text{max}}=\log(\gamma).
Thus the maximal
Lyapunov exponent delivers, through exponentiation,
the typical growth rate of perturbations to the system.

14.2
Analog Forecasting

Assume that we are given time-series data set
V^{\dagger}

as in Data Assumption
14.1
.
The goal of
forecasting

is to predict
v_{q}=\psi^{(q)}(v_{0})
given
v_{0}
, for
q\in\mathbb{N},
assuming that the map
\psi
is unknown.

Analog forecasting
, specifically, produces forecasts by identifying nearby points to
v_{0}
in the data set
V^{\dagger}
and using the evolution, in the data set, of these points to make a forecast.

14.2.1
Basic Analog Forecasting

Let
\mathsf{d}
be a distance-like deterministic scoring rule

on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
from Definition
11.81
,
recalling that any norm on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
provides
an example. A basic instantiation of
analog forecasting
works as follows. Let

(14.4)

n^{\star}\in\argmin_{n\in\mathsf{N}}\mathsf{d}(v_{0},v^{\dagger}_{n}).

Then the prediction is

(14.5)

v_{q}=v^{\dagger}_{n^{\star}+q}\,\quad q=0,\ldots,N-n^{\star}.

For
q>N-n^{\star}
no prediction is made because
the data does not support doing so.
In principle the
\argmin
operation may not deliver a unique
n^{\star}
.
The preceding discussion implies that choosing
the minimum of all
n^{\star}
will deliver the ability to make predictions over
the longest horizon.

The intuition behind the forecast is simple to articulate:
find the closest point in the data set to
v_{0}
and ask what happened
q
time units later in the data set. This is then used
as the prediction.

Theorem 14.8
.

Assume that we are given a time-series data set as in Data
Assumption
14.1
and that the time series is not constant in

n
. Then the nearest-analog map

F_{0}(v):=v^{\dagger}_{n^{\star}(v)},\qquad n^{\star}(v)\in\argmin_{n\in\mathsf{N}}\mathsf{d}(v,v^{\dagger}_{n}),

is not continuous.

Proof.

The range of
F_{0}
is contained in the finite set

\{v^{\dagger}_{0},\ldots,v^{\dagger}_{N}\}
. Moreover,
F_{0}(v^{\dagger}_{n})=v^{\dagger}_{n}
for every
n
,
and hence
F_{0}
is not constant. If
F_{0}
were continuous, its range
would be connected because
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is connected. However, a finite
connected subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
contains only one point, giving a
contradiction.
∎

For
q>0
, the forecast map may also be discontinuous: as
v_{0}
varies,
the selected nearest analog may change from
v^{\dagger}_{n}
to
v^{\dagger}_{n^{\prime}}
,
causing the prediction to jump from
v^{\dagger}_{n+q}
to
v^{\dagger}_{n^{\prime}+q}
when
these two outcomes differ.

Remark 14.9
.

Rather than forecasting from
v_{0}
one can consider the setting where one forecasts from an initial trajectory
\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{l}\}
rather than from a single point
v_{0}.
In that setting, analog forecasting proceeds by finding the closest so-called
motif
of length
l+1
in the data set, and then forecasting by considering what happened from that point onward.
Such an approach may be motivated by Takens’ embedding theorem, discussed
below in Remark
14.15
.
∎

In the next subsection we describe a generalization which resolves the undesirable
discontinuous behavior highlighted in Theorem
14.8
.

14.2.2
Kernel Analog Forecasting

The idea we deploy here to overcome the discontinuous behavior of
the simple analog forecasting method in the previous subsection is
an intuitive one: rather than predict using a single closest
point in the data set, we predict using multiple points in the
data set, but weight them
smoothly
according to their relevance
to the initial point
v_{0}
from which we wish to make a forecast.
To effect this smooth weighting we use kernels as
introduced in Definition
11.19
.

Let
c:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
be a positive definite and strictly positive

kernel
– see Definition
11.19
. Then make prediction

(14.6)

v_{q}=\sum_{n=0}^{N-q}p(v_{0},v^{\dagger}_{n})v^{\dagger}_{n+q},\quad q=0,\ldots,N,

where the weight function
p:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
normalizes the kernel evaluations
so that the weights add to one:

p(v_{0},v^{\dagger}_{n})=\frac{c(v_{0},v^{\dagger}_{n})}{\sum_{m=0}^{N-q}c(v_{0},v^{\dagger}_{m})}.

In practice this method will not work well for
q
close to
N
as the data available is then sparse.
Note that the basic analog forecast (
14.5
) only worked for
q
up to
N-n^{\star}
.

This method, too, has a simple intuitive explanation, generalizing
analog forecasting. Rather than forecasting using
q
steps ahead
from a single closest point in the data set to the initial condition,

q
-step forecasts are made from the first
(N+1-q)
points in the data set.
These are then weighted according to how close the starting point in
the data set is to
v_{0}
; the kernel performs this weighting.

Example 14.10
.

A typical choice of kernel is the radial basis function kernel

c(v,w)=\exp(-|v-w|^{2}/\ell)
. The choice of
\ell
is crucial
to the success of the methodology and should be viewed as a hyperparameter,
tuned to the specific data set at hand.
∎

Remark 14.11
.

Assuming that the kernel is continuous in its arguments, it
follows that this forecast will be continuous with respect to

v_{0}.
Note that we have only used points in the data set
from which it is possible to forecast forward
q
steps.
In fact we can only do this for a subset of points in the data
given by Data Assumption
14.1
.
Thus the effective size of the data set is
N+1-q
in (
14.6
).
∎

14.2.3
Role of Lyapunov Exponents

The success of analog forecasting depends on the
Lyapunov exponents of the system that generated

V^{\dagger}
. Recall that the analog forecast from
v_{0}
is found by identifying
n
such that
v^{\dagger}_{n}

is the closest point in the data set to
v_{0}
and setting
v_{q}=v^{\dagger}_{n+q}=\psi^{(q)}(v^{\dagger}_{n}).

Thus, approximately for
|v^{\dagger}_{n}-v_{0}|
small, we have that on average

(14.7)

|v_{q}-\psi^{(q)}(v_{0})|=|\psi^{(q)}(v^{\dagger}_{n})-\psi^{(q)}(v_{0})|\approx|v^{\dagger}_{n}-v_{0}|e^{\lambda_{\text{max}}q}.

When
\psi
is chaotic and
\lambda_{\text{max}}>0
, this leads to a potentially large error whenever
q
is large.

14.3
Recurrent Structure

As in the previous section we seek to forecast in a purely data-driven way, using
Data Assumption
14.1
. Here we attempt to forecast by learning a map consistent
with the data given in Data Assumption
14.1
, and then use that map to forecast.
In its simplest form the map is from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
into itself, and this
map can be composed with itself
q
times, and applied to any initial
point
v_{0}
, to provide a forecast
v_{q}.
This idea is explained
in Subsection
14.3.1
. The underlying
assumption in trying to predict using a map from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
into itself
is that the data is generated by a Markovian model on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If the
data is not compatible with this Markovian assumption then memory needs
to be accounted for in prediction. In Subsection
14.3.2

we explain how this memory may be accounted for by learning a Markovian model
on
\mathbb{R}^{d+r}
, where
r
itself is a hyperparameter to be learned.
Subsection
14.3.3
makes a link between
the methods introduced in Subsection
14.3.2
and
the idea of random features introduced
in Section
12.3
.

14.3.1
Markovian Prediction

Assume that the data
V^{\dagger}=\{v^{\dagger}_{n}\}_{n=0}^{N}
from Data Assumption
14.1

is the outcome of deterministic Markovian model (
14.1
), but that

\psi
is not known to us. In order to learn
\psi
we introduce parameter space

\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{\theta}}
and family of functions
\psi(\cdot\,;\theta):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
for all
\theta\in\Theta.
We then aim to choose
\theta\in\Theta
so that dynamical system

(14.8)

\displaystyle v_{n+1}=\psi(v_{n};\theta)

fits the given data
V^{\dagger}.

Function
\psi(\cdot\,;\theta):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
may be chosen, for example,
as a neural network, a random features model,
or the mean of a Gaussian process, as in Chapter
12
.

We wish to choose
\theta\in\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{\theta}}
so
that the data provided by Data Assumption
14.1
is plausibly
generated, approximately, by (
14.8
).
We can view this as a generalization of the supervised learning problem,
defined by Data Assumption
12.1
, where the inputs are
no longer i.i.d. With this connection in mind, we propose to learn
\theta

by solving the following optimization problem:

(14.9a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\bigl|v^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\psi(v^{\dagger}_{n};\theta)\big|^{2},

(14.9b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta).

This may be viewed as a supervised learning objective with
correlated
data.
Furthermore, the
v^{\dagger}_{n}
are not, in general, identically distributed unless
in the setting where
v^{\dagger}_{0}
is sampled from an invariant measure.
At first glance these two facts would appear to make the data less informative
than in the standard i.i.d. case arising in supervised learning.
However, there are additional features that result from assuming the
data comes (at least approximately) from a deterministic map on
\mathbb{R}^{d}.

In particular, as well as expecting that data points indexed by integers
separated by one are linked via
\psi(\cdot\,;\theta)
, we
also expect that data points separated by integer
m
are linked by

\psi^{(m)}(\cdot\,;\theta)
, the
m
-fold composition of
\psi(\cdot\,;\theta)

with itself. Using this idea with
m=2
suggests, for example, consideration
of the loss function, for some
\lambda>0
,

(14.10a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\bigl|v^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\psi(v^{\dagger}_{n};\theta)\big|^{2}+\lambda\frac{1}{(N-1)}\sum_{n=0}^{N-2}\bigl|v^{\dagger}_{n+2}-\psi^{(2)}(v^{\dagger}_{n};\theta)\big|^{2},

(14.10b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta).

Remark 14.12
.

There are many variants on the idea expressed in the optimization
problem (
14.10
), often referred to as
roll-out
.
For example here we have rolled out over
m=2
steps, but other choices of
roll-out duration
m
may be employed. Furthermore the roll-out can be employed
in a two-step optimization process, first solving (
14.9
) and
only then solving (
14.10
), initialized at the outcome of the first
optimization problem; this is sometimes referred to as fine-tuning.
Since neither optimization will be exact in general,
this will typically deliver a different parameter choice than that arising from
directly attempting to solve (
14.10
).

Lyapunov exponents (recall (
14.3
))
play a role
in interpreting the incorporation of roll-out. In general, the larger
m
is,
the harder it is to fit the data to
m
-fold compositions of

\psi(\cdot\,;\theta)
. In particular, for chaotic systems,
the maximal Lyapunov exponent will be larger than zero, leading to sensitivity
in maps defined by
m
-fold composition for larger
m.
The two-stage process, where
a parameter ball-park is identified, without roll-out, and then it is adjusted
to reflect a roll-out, over
m=2
or more compositions, can help in training
on chaotic systems.
∎

The population-level optimization problem that (
14.9
) approximates
may be defined as follows. Assume that
v^{\dagger}_{0}\sim\mu

is sampled from invariant measure
\mu
and let
\pi_{1}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})

be the correlation measure of the process
\{v^{\dagger}_{n}\}.
Then

(14.11a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{(v,w)\sim\pi_{1}(\cdot,\cdot)}\bigl|w-\psi(v;\theta)\big|^{2},

(14.11b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).

Furthermore, if the process
\{v^{\dagger}_{n}\}_{n=0}^{N-1}
is ergodic, then
{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)
is the pointwise limit of
{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta).

The following theorem shows that the minimizer of the population-level loss is the posterior mean
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[w|v]
. In particular, if the function that computes the posterior mean is within the class of functions parameterized by
\theta\in\Theta
, it will be recovered by minimizing
{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)
.

Theorem 14.13
.

Assume that
w
and
\psi(v)
are square-integrable. Then,

(14.12)

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(v,w)\sim\pi_{1}(\cdot,\cdot)}\Bigl[|w-\psi(v)|^{2}\Bigr]\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{(v,w)\sim\pi_{1}(\cdot,\cdot)}\Bigl[|w-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[w|v]|^{2}\Bigr].

Furthermore, the inequality is strict if
\psi(v)\neq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[w|v]
.

Proof.

This follows directly from Theorem
5.9
.
∎

If we include roll-out over two steps then the natural
population-level data assumption is that we are given, not only
\pi_{1}(v,w)

from a stationary process,
but also the correlation measure
\pi_{2}(v,w)
in
