\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
of the same process.
The limiting optimization problem is

(14.13a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{(u,v)\sim\pi_{1}(\cdot,\cdot)}\bigl|v-\psi(u;\theta)\big|^{2}+\lambda\mathbb{E}^{(u,w)\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot,\cdot)}\bigl|w-\psi^{(2)}(u;\theta)\big|^{2},

(14.13b)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}(\theta).

These ideas can be readily generalized to roll-out over
m
steps.

14.3.2
Memory and Prediction

The premise of the preceding subsection is that
data in Data Assumption
14.1
derives from a Markov model,
such as (
14.1
), but there are numerous settings
where this is not the case, even approximately. Attempting to
fit a model of the form (
14.8
), using optimization
problems such as (
14.9
) or (
14.10
), will typically
lead to large values of the objective function at the optimum, and to
poor predictions, when the data is not consistent with a Markovian
model. In such a setting it is of interest to introduce
an unobserved latent variable
\{r_{n}\}_{n=0}^{N-1}
, with

r_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
, and then to hypothesize a Markov model
for the pair
(v_{n},r_{n})
of the form

(14.14a)

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\psi(v_{n},r_{n};\theta_{v}),

(14.14b)

\displaystyle r_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\varphi(v_{n},r_{n};\theta_{r}),\quad r_{0}=\theta_{0}.

If
\psi,\varphi
are neural networks then the overall structure is known as a
recurrent neural network
, or RNN for short.
We define
\theta:=(\theta_{v},\theta_{r},\theta_{0})\in\Theta:=\Theta_{v}\times\Theta_{r}\times\Theta_{0}
and
learn
\theta
by solving the minimization problem

(14.15a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\bigl|v^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\psi(v^{\dagger}_{n},r_{n};\theta_{v})\big|^{2},

(14.15b)

\displaystyle r_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\varphi(v^{\dagger}_{n},r_{n};\theta_{r}),\,r_{0}=\theta_{0},

(14.15c)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta).

The key fact about (
14.14
b) is that, once
(\theta_{r},\theta_{0})
are chosen
it defines a unique function, for each

n
, taking the history
\{v_{\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{n-1}
into
r_{n}:

(14.16)

r_{n}=\mathcal{F}_{n}\bigl(\{v_{m}\}_{m=0}^{n-1};\theta_{r},\theta_{0}).

Thus (
14.14
a) becomes memory dependent as a closed model for the evolution of
v_{n}
. Nonetheless
we retain the benefits of being Markovian with respect to the pair
(v_{n},r_{n}).

Remark 14.14
.

Sometimes
r_{0}
is found using data assimilation.
The observations comprise elements of the sequence
\{v_{n}\}
and the unobserved sequences to
be learned are components of
\{r_{n}\}
and, in particular, the initial condition. Indeed there is a connection here
to the expectation maximization algorithm from Section
15.5
, and its application in Chapter
8
.
∎

Remark 14.15
.

The need for memory in time-series prediction can be motivated not only for non-Markovian systems, but also for Markovian but partially observed systems. One way to see this is using
Takens’ embedding theorem
, which says (roughly) that under quite general conditions, given only partial observations of a Markovian system, compensated for by employing enough memory, one can reconstruct the original dynamics.

We state Takens’ Theorem informally; see the bibliography for references.
Suppose that
\psi:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
is a smooth, invertible map on a compact

d
-dimensional state space
\mathcal{M}
, defining the deterministic dynamical
system
v^{\dagger}_{j+1}=\psi(v^{\dagger}_{j})
. Let
h:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}

be a smooth scalar-valued observation function and define vector
Y^{\dagger}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
by

Y^{\dagger}_{j}:=\Bigl(h(v^{\dagger}_{j-(m-1)}),h(v^{\dagger}_{j-(m-2)}),\ldots,h(v^{\dagger}_{j})\Bigr).

Provided
m
is large enough, Takens’ Theorem states that for typical
smooth observation functions
h
the delay-coordinate map

v^{\dagger}_{j}\mapsto Y^{\dagger}_{j}
is a smooth embedding; informally, this excludes
exceptional observation functions whose delayed values fail to
distinguish different states. It follows that

Y^{\dagger}_{j+1}=\Psi(Y^{\dagger}_{j})
for some map
\Psi
defined on the set of delay
vectors, and that this new dynamical system is equivalent to the
original one in the sense that they are related by a smooth, invertible
change of coordinates.
∎

14.3.3
Memory and Reservoir Computing

Including memory is important in many time-series prediction tasks,
as the data may not come from a Markov process. However, learning
RNN structure can be challenging for two reasons: (i) because
r_{n}
is an unobserved
latent variable; and (ii) because, within the context of gradient-based methods (Chapter
15
),
propagation of gradients of
(\theta_{r},\theta_{0})
through the model is difficult.

Reservoir computing
is one approach to overcome this. The model deployed is
again (
14.14
); however, rather than learning

(\theta_{r},\theta_{0})
, that parameter is simply picked at random from some probability
measure on space of appropriate dimension and the following
optimization problem is solved:

(14.17a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta_{v})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\bigl|v^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\psi(v^{\dagger}_{n},r_{n};\theta_{v})\big|^{2},

(14.17b)

\displaystyle r_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\varphi(v^{\dagger}_{n},r_{n};\theta_{r}),\,r_{0}=\theta_{0},

(14.17c)

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}_{v}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{\theta_{v}\in\Theta_{v}}{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta_{v}).

Crucially the optimization is only over
\theta_{v}
, as

(\theta_{r},\theta_{0})
is fixed at a randomly chosen value.

Remark 14.16
.

Typically the method requires
r
of higher dimension than
for the RNN in order to be successful. This is related to
the issue of how expressive randomly chosen functions are.
Indeed, there is a connection to the random features methodology of Section
12.3
. To see
this, recall that equation (
14.17
b) reveals that

r_{n}
is defined by (
14.16
).
Thus, once
(\theta_{r},\theta_{0})
is chosen at random,
r_{n}

is a fixed function of the history of the observed data up to time
n-1.

∎

14.4
Autoregressive Models

The models we have fit to data in Sections
14.2
and
14.3
are all deterministic. Here we
consider learning stochastic models to explain the data.
We continue to work under Data Assumption
14.1
.
To this end, consider the stochastic dynamical system, for sequences
\{v_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(14.18)

v_{n+1}=\Psi\bigl(\{v_{n-\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{p-1}\bigr)+\varepsilon_{n},\qquad n\geq p-1,

where
\varepsilon_{n}\sim\nu
i.i.d. for centered
\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\Psi:\mathbb{R}^{pd}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a deterministic mapping. Generating a sequence requires specification of
\{v_{\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{p-1}
.

Remark 14.17
.

When
equipped with initialization of
\{v_{\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{p-1}
, equation (
14.18
) defines a
Markov process on
\mathbb{R}^{pd}
for variable

(14.19)

V_{n}:=\{v_{n+\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{p-1},\qquad n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}.

However we will not use this formulation on
\mathbb{R}^{pd}
explicitly in what follows.
∎

To link with Data Assumption
14.1
we consider a sequence defined for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and note the following
autoregressive factorization of the probability of such a sequence under
the dynamics model (
14.18
):

(14.20a)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\{v_{n}\}_{n=0}^{N}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\Bigl[\prod_{n=p}^{N}\,\mathbb{P}\bigl(v_{n}|\{v_{\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{n-1}\bigr)\Bigr]\mathbb{P}\bigl(\{v_{m}\}_{m=0}^{p-1})

(14.20b)

\displaystyle=\Bigl[\prod_{n=p}^{N}\,\mathbb{P}\bigl(v_{n}|\{v_{\ell}\}_{\ell=n-p}^{n-1}\bigr)\Bigr]\mathbb{P}\bigl(\{v_{m}\}_{m=0}^{p-1})

(14.20c)

\displaystyle=\Bigl[\prod_{n=p}^{N}\,\nu\Bigl(v_{n}-\Psi\bigl(\{v_{n-1-\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{p-1}\bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr]\mathbb{P}\bigl(\{v_{m}\}_{m=0}^{p-1}\bigr).

The model (
14.18
) is readily generalized to noise that is not additive, and models of the form

(14.21)

v_{n+1}=\Psi\bigl(\{v_{n-\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{p-1},\varepsilon_{n}\bigr).

In this case (
14.20
b) still holds; identity (
14.20
c) however, is specific to additive noise.

In Subsection
14.4.1
we introduce linear autoregressive models as a special case of the autoregressive model (
14.18
). Then, in Subsection
14.4.2
, we introduce a transport-based generalization of autoregressive models.

14.4.1
Linear Autoregressive Models

In this subsection we consider the vector autoregressive
(VAR) process:

Definition 14.18
.

The
p
th-order VAR model, written VAR(
p
), is

(14.22)

v_{n+1}=c+\sum_{\ell=0}^{p-1}A_{\ell}\,v_{n-\ell}+\varepsilon_{n},

where the
A_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
are fixed matrices,
c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a fixed vector, and

\varepsilon_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C)
is an i.i.d. mean-zero Gaussian sequence
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with fixed covariance
C\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\textrm{sym},>}.

∎

Given initial conditions
\{v_{n}\}_{n=0}^{p-1}
this will generate
\{v_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
, an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued sequence.
As well as generalizing the foregoing sections by allowing for
stochasticity in the dynamics, this model also generalizes what we
have seen in previous sections in its treatment of memory.
In Subsection
14.3.1
we employed a deterministic Markovian model
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with no memory. And in Subsection
14.3.2

we employed
a model which, when viewed as a dynamics model in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, had memory
that, for each
n
, reaches all the way back to time
0
– this is demonstrated
in (
14.16
). The VAR process has memory, if
p>1
, but the
memory is over a fixed finite window, the same for every
n
and
determined by
p
– the memory does not stretch back all the way
to time
0.

Remark 14.19
.

When
v_{n}
is a scalar, the process is known simply as an
autoregressive (AR) model.
It is sometimes useful to consider
\varepsilon_{n}
to be
a non-Gaussian strictly stationary and ergodic process, but we
restrict to the i.i.d. Gaussian model here for simplicity.
∎

In the following we take
c=0
for simplicity. But the methodological
developments are readily modified to accommodate the setting
c\neq 0
; the resulting optimization problem is
still quadratic.
We also assume that
C
is known and positive definite, so that
the unknown parameters of the model are
\theta:=(A_{0},\ldots,A_{p-1}).

Assume that, for
m=0,\ldots,p-1
, the
v_{m}
are i.i.d. random variables with
distribution
v_{m}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\gamma^{2}I),
for some
\gamma>0.

Then, using the data
V^{\dagger}=\{v^{\dagger}_{n}\}_{n=0}^{N}
given in Data Assumption
14.1
,
define the objective function

(14.23)

\mathsf{L}^{N}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=p}^{N}\bigl|C^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bigl(v^{\dagger}_{n}-\sum_{\ell=0}^{p-1}A_{\ell}v^{\dagger}_{n-\ell-1}\bigr)\big|^{2}+\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}\sum_{m=0}^{p-1}|v^{\dagger}_{m}|^{2}

noting that then, by (
14.20
), the likelihood
\mathbb{P}\bigl(\{v^{\dagger}_{n}\}_{n=0}^{N}|\theta\bigr)\propto\exp\bigl(-\mathsf{L}^{N}(\theta)\bigr).

Thus maximum likelihood estimation is defined by the quadratic optimization problem

(14.24)

\theta^{\star}\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathsf{L}^{N}(\theta).

Remark 14.20
.

Learning matrix
C
, in addition to the matrices
\{A_{\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{p-1}
, is also possible,
but leads to a non-quadratic objective. Dependence on the normalization constant of the likelihood
on
\det C
must be included in the loss function.
∎

Remark 14.21
.

If we assume that the data is generated by a strictly stationary process
then there is a population-level limit of the minimization problem defined by
N^{-1}\mathsf{L}^{N}.

It may be expressed in terms of the distribution of the underlying stationary
process on the data. We omit details, but conclude with an example of
a strictly stationary process of VAR type.
∎

Example 14.22
.

Consider the following VAR(1) process with
c=0
and defined by matrices
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
C\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\textrm{sym},>}
:

v_{n+1}=Av_{n}+\varepsilon_{n},\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},

where
\varepsilon_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C)
i.i.d. .
Assume that
A
is stable, meaning that all its eigenvalues have modulus
strictly less than one. Then the Lyapunov equation

C_{\infty}=AC_{\infty}A^{\top}+C

has a unique positive definite solution
C_{\infty}
. If

v_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C_{\infty})
independently of the i.i.d. noise sequence

\{\varepsilon_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
, then the process is strictly stationary.
Indeed, then,
v_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C_{\infty})
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}.

To establish these facts we first note that the linear additive Gaussian structure implies by
induction that if
v_{0}
is Gaussian, then
v_{n}

is Gaussian for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}.
Assume that

v_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(m_{0},C_{0}).
Then
v_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(m_{n},C_{n})
,
and the mean and covariance update according to

(14.25a)

\displaystyle m_{n+1}

\displaystyle=Am_{n},

(14.25b)

\displaystyle C_{n+1}

\displaystyle=AC_{n}A^{\top}+C.

The pair
(0,C_{\infty})
is a fixed point of this iterated map on mean and covariance.
Thus we have established that, if
v_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C_{\infty})

then
v_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C_{\infty})
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}.

The vector
(v_{n},\ldots,v_{n+p})
will then also be jointly Gaussian
with mean zero. To establish strict stationarity it suffices to show
that the covariance of
(v_{n},\ldots,v_{n+p})
is independent of
n
, for
any
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}.
To do this it is sufficient to show that
the covariance of
v_{n}
with
v_{n+m}
is
n
-independent, for any

m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}.
We note that, by induction,

v_{n+m}=A^{m}v_{n}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}A^{m-j}\varepsilon_{n+j-1}.

Because the sequence
\{\varepsilon_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is i.i.d. it follows that, for
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[v_{n+m}v_{n}^{\top}]

\displaystyle=A^{m}\mathbb{E}[v_{n}v_{n}^{\top}]+\sum_{j=1}^{m}A^{m-j}\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{n+j-1}v_{n}^{\top}],

\displaystyle=A^{m}C_{\infty}.

As desired this depends only on
m
and not on
n
and the desired result is
established.
∎

Remark 14.23
.

The dynamics and observation processes underlying the steady-state Kalman filter (Subsection
6.3.3
) are VAR(1) processes. Under observability and controllability conditions, moreover, the steady-state Kalman filter itself (
6.22
) is a strictly stationary VAR(1) process as in Example
14.22
. See the bibliography Section
14.5
.
∎

14.4.2
Transport-Based Autoregressive Models

In this section we extend the linear autoregressive models with additive
Gaussian noise, from Subsection
14.4.1
, to
a more general nonlinear and non-Gaussian probabilistic form.
We do this by modeling non-Gaussian distributions
as the pushforward of a Gaussian variable. This is an idea
we introduced through transport,
in Section
13.2
, in the context of
generative modelling.

To motivate the approach we adopt, we first recall the VAR(
p
) process (
14.22
) from
Subsection
14.4.1
. If
(c,C)
are assumed known, and we define

\theta=(A_{0},\ldots,A_{p-1}),
then the model can be formulated through use of a conditional
probability kernel
\pi
of the form

(14.26)

v_{n+1}\sim\pi(\cdot|v_{n},\ldots,v_{n-p+1};\theta)=\mathcal{N}\Bigl(c+\sum_{\ell=0}^{p-1}A_{\ell}\,v_{n-\ell},C\Bigr).

Furthermore, the independent noise structure assumed in
Subsection
14.4.1
implies the order-
p

Markov property

\mathbb{P}\bigl(v_{n+1}|v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}\bigr)=\mathbb{P}\bigl(v_{n+1}|v_{n-p+1},\ldots,v_{n}\bigr).

We generalize the linear Gaussian structure of VAR(
p
) in what follows.
We let
\varrho(v)
denote the standard Gaussian density on
\mathbb{R}^{d}.
We introduce a transport
T(\cdot\,;V,\theta):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, depending on
V\in\mathbb{R}^{pd}
and parameters
\theta\in\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
, so that

(14.27)

v_{n+1}\sim\pi(\cdot|v_{n},\ldots,v_{n-p+1};\theta)=T(\cdot\,;v_{n},\ldots,v_{n-p+1},\theta)_{\sharp}\varrho.

We assume that the randomness used at different time indices is
independent; equivalently, the model may be written as

v_{n+1}=T\bigl(z_{n};v_{n},\ldots,v_{n-p+1},\theta\bigr),\qquad z_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\varrho.

Example 14.24
.

The VAR (
14.26
) falls into the general class (
14.27
) with
T
given by

T(z;v_{n},\ldots,v_{n-p+1},\theta)=c+\sum_{\ell=0}^{p-1}A_{\ell}\,v_{n-\ell}+C^{1/2}z,

where, if
(c,C)
are assumed known,
\theta=(A_{0},\ldots,A_{p-1}).

∎

Remark 14.25
.

Note that (
14.27
) postulates an autoregressive structure that subsumes (
14.18
)
as a special case. Furthermore, in the case where the
\varepsilon_{n}
are Gaussian, (
14.21
)
provides a state-space formulation of the Markov chain on
the augmented state-space
\mathbb{R}^{pd}
(see (
14.33
))
implied by (
14.27
). We also note that the identity
(
14.27
) can be used in expression (
14.20
b)
to define the conditional probabilities arising there.
∎

In Subsection
14.4.1
we showed how to learn parameters
\theta
for the VAR(
p
) linear
and Gaussian model, by invoking a maximum
likelihood principle, leading to an optimization problem for
\theta.

We now aim to choose parameter
\theta
so that the more general model (
14.27
)
explains the data given in Data Assumption
14.1
, again using a maximum likelihood approach. We assume that

T(\cdot\,;V,\theta):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is invertible for all
(V,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{pd}\times\Theta,

with inverse
S(\cdot\,;V,\theta):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Map
T(\cdot\,;V,\theta)
takes as input
z
in the latent space (which is

\mathbb{R}^{d}
in this case); inverse
S(\cdot\,;V,\theta)
takes as input
v
in the original space
\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Then we can use the change-of-variables formula from Lemma
11.45
to express the density of the transformed variable as

(14.28)

\displaystyle\pi(v_{n+1}|v_{n},\ldots,v_{n-p+1};\theta)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad=\varrho\bigl(S(v_{n+1};v_{n},\ldots,v_{n-p+1},\theta)\bigr)\det D_{v}S\bigl(\cdot\,;v_{n},\ldots,v_{n-p+1},\theta\bigr)|_{v_{n+1}}.

This expression is useful for formulating an optimization problem to determine the choice of
\theta.

To simplify the exposition we continue our discussion of transport-based learning
of probabilistic models in the restricted setting where
p=1.

In this case, our goal is to find invertible transport
T(\cdot\,;V,\theta):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with inverse
S(\cdot\,;V,\theta):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, so that
\pi(v_{n+1}|v_{n},\theta)\coloneqq T(\cdot\,;v_{n},\theta)_{\sharp}\varrho(v_{n+1})
models the conditional distribution
implied by the data from Data Assumption
14.1
.
To this end we work with function classes
\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}
that satisfy the inclusion

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}\subset\Bigl\{T:\;

\displaystyle T(\cdot\,;w,\theta)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\text{ is invertible for every }(w,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta,

(14.29)

\displaystyle\det D_{z}T(z;w,\theta)>0\quad\text{for every }(z,w,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta\Bigr\}.

The following result, which provides a canonical working example in the
p=1
setting,
follows directly from (
14.28
), using the fact that
\varrho
is a standard Gaussian:

Proposition 14.26
.

Consider the probabilistic model (
14.27
) in the case where
p=1.

Define
T(z;w,\theta):=\psi(w;\theta)+C^{1/2}z,
for some class of maps
\psi(\cdot\,;\theta)\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
all
\theta\in\Theta
and for matrix
C\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\mathrm{sym},>}
. Then we have

\displaystyle S(v;w,\theta)

\displaystyle=C^{-1/2}\bigl(v-\psi(w;\theta)\bigr),

\displaystyle\pi(v|w;\theta)

\displaystyle=\varrho\Bigl(C^{-1/2}\bigl(v-\psi(w;\theta)\bigr)\Bigr)\det C^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d}\,\det{C}}}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{2}|v-\psi(w;\theta)|_{C}^{2}}}\right).

That is, the conditional distribution for the next state
v_{n+1}
,
\pi(\cdot|v_{n};\theta)
, is a multivariate Gaussian of the form
\mathcal{N}\bigl(\psi(v_{n}),C\bigr)
.

Example 14.27
.

In setting of the preceding proposition we may, for example,
choose
\psi(\cdot\,;\theta)
to be from a family of neural networks
of specified depth and widths; see Remark
12.6
.
∎

Given a sequence of data, we can learn the transport parameters by maximizing the log-likelihood of consecutive data
pairs
\{(v^{\dagger}_{n},v^{\dagger}_{n+1})\}_{n=0}^{N-1}
under the model. From (
14.28
) we obtain, in case
p=1
,

(14.30)

\displaystyle\pi(v_{n+1}|v_{n};\theta)

\displaystyle=\varrho\bigl(S(v_{n+1};v_{n},\theta)\bigr)\det D_{v}S(v_{n+1};v_{n},\theta).

Because of the assumed conditional independence structure,
\pi(v_{n+1}|v_{n},\theta)
defines
a Markov chain on
\mathbb{R}^{d}.
If we assume that
v^{\dagger}_{0}\sim p_{0}
then the probability of sequence

\{v^{\dagger}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathsf{N}}
from Data Assumption
14.1
is, using the Markov structure,
given by

\mathbb{P}\bigl(\{v^{\dagger}_{n}\}_{n=0}^{N}|\theta\bigr)=\Bigl(\prod_{n=0}^{N-1}\pi(v_{n+1}^{\dagger}|v_{n}^{\dagger};\theta)\Bigr)p_{0}(v^{\dagger}_{0}).

This is a particular case of (
14.20
). We again assume a standard Gaussian reference
\varrho
.
We also assume that
p_{0}
does not depend on
\theta.
Then, up to a
\theta
-independent constant, the
negative log-likelihood
\mathsf{L}^{N}(\theta)={\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta)+\mathsf{const}
, where

(14.31a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\log\pi(v_{n+1}^{\dagger}|v_{n}^{\dagger};\theta)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log\varrho\bigl(S(v_{n+1}^{\dagger};v_{n}^{\dagger},\theta)\bigr)+\log\det D_{v}S(v_{n+1}^{\dagger};v_{n}^{\dagger},\theta)}}\right]

(14.31b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{2}|S(v_{n+1}^{\dagger};v_{n}^{\dagger},\theta)|^{2}-\log\det D_{v}S(v_{n+1}^{\dagger};v_{n}^{\dagger},\theta)}}\right]+\mathsf{const},

where
\mathsf{const}
is a constant that is again independent of
\theta
.
The optimal transport parameters are then given by

\theta^{\star}\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta).

Remark 14.28
.

Here we have assumed that
p_{0}
is
\theta
-independent. Another natural situation is
where
p_{0}
is the invariant measure associated with the process giving rise to
data in Data Assumption
14.1
. In this case
p_{0}
depends on
\theta
and
the estimation problem is more complicated. Relatedly, it is also natural to consider
the population loss limit of
N^{-1}{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\cdot)
, using an ergodic invariant measure.
∎

Example 14.29
.

A commonly used non-Gaussian model is based on a nonlinear diagonal transformation of the form

S(v_{n+1};v_{n},\theta)=s(v_{n+1}-\psi(v_{n};\theta);\theta),

where
s(\cdot\,;\theta):\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an invertible function, with inverse

s^{-1}(\cdot\,;\theta):\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for all
\theta\in\Theta
, and
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
. This can be re-written in terms of the direct transport as

T(z;v_{n},\theta)=\psi(v_{n};\theta)+s^{-1}(z;\theta).

Recall that
z\sim\varrho
, the standard Gaussian; thus
v_{n+1}
is
obtained by applying
\psi
to
v_{n}
and adding the generally
non-Gaussian innovation
s^{-1}(z;\theta)
. When
s
acts
component-wise and monotonically, the distribution of this innovation
is an example of a
nonparanormal
, or

Gaussian copula
, distribution.
∎

Example 14.30
.

Assume that
S(v_{n+1};v_{n},\theta)=v_{n+1}-\psi(v_{n};\theta),
as in Proposition
14.26
, with
C=I
. Then we have

D_{v_{n+1}}S(v_{n+1};v_{n},\theta)=I
. Thus, the learning problem based on the loss function in (
14.31b
) reduces to

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}\in\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|v_{n+1}^{\dagger}-\psi(v_{n}^{\dagger};\theta)|^{2}}}\right].

That is, we can identify the model parameters by solving a supervised learning problem for the nonlinear map
\psi
. This is the setting of Subsection
14.3.1
. The scaling factor
N^{-1}
does not change the optimization problem, but makes clear that under ergodicity assumptions there is a population loss optimization problem of
the form (
14.11
).
∎

Remark 14.31
.

In Chapter
4

we show how to model non-Gaussian distributions, also using
transport, in the context of Bayesian inverse problems.
∎

14.4.3
Learning the Conditional Distribution Using Strictly Proper Scoring Rules

In the preceding Subsection
14.4.2
we studied the use of transport maps to approximate

(14.32)

\pi(v_{n+1}|V^{\dagger}_{n}):=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v_{n+1}|V^{\dagger}_{n})

where, recall,
V^{\dagger}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{pd}
is defined by
1
1

1

Note that the indexing differs from that used to define
V_{n}

in (
14.19
).

(14.33)

V^{\dagger}_{n}:=\{v^{\dagger}_{n-\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{p-1},\qquad n=p-1,\ldots,N.

We learned parameters of the maps using maximum likelihood estimation.
In this subsection we learn the same conditional distribution
using scoring rules to define the objective function.
The following theorem provides the basis for learning the conditional
\pi
using strictly proper scoring rules; recall their definition in Section
11.3
.

Theorem 14.32
.

Consider a strictly proper scoring rule
\mathsf{S}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, and let
\pi(v_{n+1}|V^{\dagger}_{n})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be the distribution
defined by (
14.32
). For any distribution
q(\cdot;V^{\dagger}_{n})
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, parameterized by
V^{\dagger}_{n}
, we have

(14.34)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{V^{\dagger}_{n}}\mathbb{E}^{v_{n+1}\sim\pi(\cdot\mid V^{\dagger}_{n})}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}\bigl(q(\cdot;V^{\dagger}_{n}),v_{n+1}\bigr)\Bigr]\geq\mathbb{E}^{V^{\dagger}_{n}}\mathbb{E}^{v_{n+1}\sim\pi(\cdot\mid V^{\dagger}_{n})}\Bigl[\mathsf{S}\bigl(\pi(\cdot\mid V^{\dagger}_{n}),v_{n+1}\bigr)\Bigr].

Furthermore, the inequality is strict if

\mathbb{P}^{V^{\dagger}_{n}}\Bigl(q(\cdot;V^{\dagger}_{n})\neq\pi(\cdot\mid V^{\dagger}_{n})\Bigr)>0.

Proof.

This follows directly from Theorem
5.20
.
∎

Now we use the objective function implied by (
14.34
) as the basis of an implementable algorithm,
under Data Assumption
14.1
. Approximating
\pi(\cdot|V^{\dagger}_{n})
by
\pi(\cdot|V^{\dagger}_{n};\theta)
and making
an empirical approximation in time, justified by assuming ergodicity, we obtain

(14.35)

{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(\theta)=\frac{1}{N-p+1}\sum_{n=p-1}^{N-1}\mathsf{S}(\pi(\cdot|V^{\dagger}_{n};\theta),v^{\dagger}_{n+1})

for some choice of strictly proper scoring rule
\mathsf{S}
.

Remark 14.33
.

Note that the transport-based conditional distributions (
14.27
) are a possible way of parameterizing
\pi(v_{n+1}|V^{\dagger}_{n};\theta)
; however, other classes of distributions can also be considered.
∎

Remark 14.34
.

Consider the case
p=1.

Then the log-likelihood cost function (
14.31a
) considered in Subsection
14.4.2
is a special case of (
14.35
), when
\mathsf{S}
is chosen to be the logarithmic score from Definition
11.70
.
∎

Remark 14.35
.

Suppose the process underlying the time-series
V^{\dagger}
is a hidden Markov model with a hidden state
z
, i.e.,

\displaystyle z_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(z_{n})+\xi_{n},\quad\xi_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma),

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=h(z_{n+1})+\eta_{n+1},\quad\eta_{n+1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Gamma),

\displaystyle z_{0}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}(m_{0},C_{0}),

as in (
6.1
), (
6.2
) (note shift of notation to align with that arising in this subsection). Then, a structure similar to (
14.32
) arises; however
V^{\dagger}_{n}
has a different definition, with dimension depending on
n
.
Now
V^{\dagger}_{n}
is given by (
6.3
) as
V^{\dagger}_{n}:=\{v^{\dagger}_{0},\ldots,v^{\dagger}_{n}\}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)d}
, to be contrasted with the fixed lag
definition of
V^{\dagger}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{pd}
given by (
14.33
), and adopted in the discussion preceding this remark.

We now discuss how, with this modified
V^{\dagger}_{n}
, the conditional probability distribution given by (
14.32
)
can be approximated using filtering algorithms.
In particular, suppose one has obtained the predicted distribution
\widehat{\pi}_{n+1}(z_{n+1}):=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(z_{n+1}|V^{\dagger}_{n})
; see the definitions in Subsection
6.2.1
. Then, (
14.32
) can be obtained from
\widehat{\pi}_{n+1}
by application of the stochastic observation map. That is, using convolution and pushforward,

(14.36)

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(v_{n+1}|V^{\dagger}_{n})=\mathcal{N}(0,\Gamma)*h_{\sharp}\widehat{\pi}_{n+1}.

In the context where the time-series has such a hidden Markov structure it is sometimes of interest to predict
v_{n+1}
from
V^{\dagger}_{n}
directly, bypassing the prediction–analysis structure of filtering (see Subsection
6.2.1
); this may be the case, for example, when a good approximation for
\Psi
is not available. This is sometimes called
end-to-end prediction
or
direct observation prediction
.
∎

14.5
Bibliography

An extensive introduction to linear autoregressive processes, including the conditions for stationarity and ergodicity, is provided in
[
226
]
.
The Lorenz ’63 model is proved to be ergodic in

[
477
,
478
]
providing a useful example. Indeed, in this context central limit theorems have been proved
[
248
]
,
characterizing the fluctuations about the law of large numbers limit in (
14.2
).

Analog forecasting was first introduced by Lorenz in
[
324
]
. A historical overview of analog forecasting and extensions, as well as estimates of error scaling, is given in
[
166
]
. The kernel analog
regularization of Lorenz’s idea was introduced and
then studied in
[
199
,
15
]
; it has recently
been extended to an efficient streaming framework
[
198
]
.

The key message contained in Subsection
14.2.3
is that purely data-driven
methods of this type will be limited by sensitivity to initial conditions.
In contrast, the data assimilation methods,
which combine data with knowledge of the model, can overcome
sensitivity to initialization; the mechanism by which this occurs
is explained in
[
423
]
and in
[
349
]

and also underlies Theorem
6.37
concerning the effect of
model error (rather than the effect of initialization error).

Memory in the context of learning model error is discussed in
[
312
]
. Reservoir computers were considered for predicting chaotic systems in
[
377
]
. Takens’ Theorem and other embedding theorems are reviewed in
[
432
]
.
The Remark
14.14
highlights the use of data assimilation in training data-driven
models. Incorporating data assimilation into the training process can be very useful, especially when learning from noisy observations and with unobserved latent variables; see Chapter
8
and the bibliography therein. Furthermore, if available, it is often beneficial to train on analyses produced by an imperfect model rather than on observations directly; see Subsection
6.4.5
and
[
79
]
.

The stationarity of the linear autoregressive model referred to in Remark
14.23
is established, under
controllability and observability conditions, by study of matrix Riccati equations – see
[
298
]
.
General non-linear autoregressive models have been considered for time-series modeling using various probabilistic models, such as conditional normalizing flows, in
[
366
,
404
]
. Some of these models rely on recurrent neural networks architectures that can summarize the past states and extract relevant features for making predictions. Learning probabilistic time-series forecasts using scoring rules was considered in
[
371
]
.

Chapter 15
Optimization and Sampling

This chapter provides an introduction to various topics in optimization and sampling that are particularly pertinent to machine learning, inverse problems and data assimilation.
We start in Section
15.1
with definitions and background results that will be useful throughout this chapter.
Section
15.2
is concerned with gradient-based
optimization, including deterministic and stochastic
gradient descent. In Section
15.3
we discuss Newton and Gauss–Newton optimization methods for nonlinear least squares; these algorithms use full or partial Hessian information to define a preconditioner and thereby accelerate the convergence.
Section
15.4
describes derivative-free ensemble Kalman methods for nonlinear least squares; these methods, which are intimately related to ensemble Kalman filters for data assimilation studied in Chapter
6
, rely on the first two empirical moments of an ensemble of particles to determine the search direction and to define a preconditioner.
Section
15.5
is devoted to expectation maximization, an important optimization algorithm for computing maximum likelihood estimators when the likelihood is intractable but can be expressed as the marginal of a tractable extended likelihood. Section
15.6
describes the Metropolis–Hastings algorithm and Gibbs samplers, two Markov chain Monte Carlo algorithms to produce approximate samples from a target distribution.
Finally, since many optimization and sampling algorithms involve derivatives of the objective, and since computing these derivatives can be a limiting computational bottleneck, in Section
15.7

we study auto-differentiation. These techniques have greatly contributed to the scalability and efficiency of modern optimization and sampling algorithms in machine learning and related tasks. Other topics in optimization and sampling are referenced in the bibliography Section
15.8
.

15.1
Background Definitions and Results

In this section we introduce some definitions
that will be useful in later sections and in other chapters.

Definition 15.1
.

A function
{\mathsf{J}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is called
convex
if, for all
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and for all
\theta\in[0,1]
, we have that

{\mathsf{J}}(\theta u+(1-\theta)v)\leq\theta{\mathsf{J}}(u)+(1-\theta){\mathsf{J}}(v).

It is called
strictly convex
if the inequality is strict whenever
u\neq v
and
\theta\in(0,1)
. For
\mu\geq 0
, function
{\mathsf{J}}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
is called
\mu
-strongly convex
if, for all
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

{\mathsf{J}}(v)\geq{\mathsf{J}}(u)+\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u),v-u\rangle+\frac{\mu}{2}|v-u|^{2}.

∎

Notice that for
\mu=0
,
\mu
-strong convexity is equivalent to convexity, while

\mu
-strong convexity with
\mu>0
implies strict convexity.
An important consequence of strict convexity is uniqueness of minimizers:

Theorem 15.2
.

Let
{\mathsf{J}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be strictly convex.
If
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are minimizers of
{\mathsf{J}},
then
u_{1}=u_{2}.

Proof.

Suppose for contradiction that
u_{1}\neq u_{2}
are distinct minimizers, and let
{\mathsf{J}}_{\rm min}:={\mathsf{J}}(u_{1})={\mathsf{J}}(u_{2}).
Let
\theta\in(0,1)
and consider the intermediate point
u_{\theta}:=\theta u_{1}+(1-\theta)u_{2}.
By strict convexity, since
u_{1}\neq u_{2}
we have

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(u_{\theta})

\displaystyle={\mathsf{J}}(\theta u_{1}+(1-\theta)u_{2})<\theta{\mathsf{J}}(u_{1})+(1-\theta){\mathsf{J}}(u_{2})={\mathsf{J}}_{\rm min}.

This contradicts the fact that
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are minimizers of
{\mathsf{J}}.

∎

The following result provides a useful equivalent characterization of
\mu
-strong convexity.

Theorem 15.3
.

Let
{\mathsf{J}}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}).
The following are equivalent:

1.

{\mathsf{J}}
is
\mu
-strongly convex.

2.

For all
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
it holds that

(15.1)

\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u)-D{\mathsf{J}}(v),u-v\rangle\geq\mu|u-v|^{2}.

Proof.

Suppose first that
{\mathsf{J}}
is
\mu
-strongly convex. Then

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(v)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathsf{J}}(u)+\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u),v-u\rangle+\frac{\mu}{2}|v-u|^{2},

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(u)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathsf{J}}(v)+\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(v),u-v\rangle+\frac{\mu}{2}|u-v|^{2}.

Adding these inequalities, we obtain that

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(v)+{\mathsf{J}}(u)\geq{\mathsf{J}}(u)+{\mathsf{J}}(v)+\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u),v-u\rangle+\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(v),u-v\rangle+\mu|u-v|^{2}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle 0\geq\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u),v-u\rangle+\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(v),u-v\rangle+\mu|u-v|^{2},

which implies (
15.1
).

Conversely, suppose that (
15.1
) holds for all
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Fix
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Then by the fundamental theorem of calculus, we have

(15.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathsf{J}}(v)-{\mathsf{J}}(u)&=\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\langle D{\mathsf{J}}\bigl(u+\theta(v-u)\bigr),v-u\bigr\rangle d\theta\\
&=\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u),v-u\rangle+\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\langle D{\mathsf{J}}\bigl(u+\theta(v-u)\bigr)-D{\mathsf{J}}(u),v-u\bigr\rangle d\theta.\end{split}

Let
x:=u+\theta(v-u)
and
y:=u.
Applying (
15.1
) with
(u,v)\mapsto(x,y)
gives

\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u+\theta(v-u))-D{\mathsf{J}}(u),\theta(v-u)\rangle\geq\mu\theta^{2}|v-u|^{2}.

Thus, for
\theta\in(0,1),

\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u+\theta(v-u))-D{\mathsf{J}}(u),v-u\rangle\geq\mu\theta|v-u|^{2}.

This implies that

\int_{0}^{1}\langle D{\mathsf{J}}\bigl(u+\theta(v-u)\bigr)-D{\mathsf{J}}\bigl(u\bigr),v-u\rangle d\theta\geq\mu|v-u|^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\theta d\theta=\frac{\mu}{2}|v-u|^{2}.

Substituting this bound into (
15.2
), we conclude that

{\mathsf{J}}(v)\geq{\mathsf{J}}(u)+\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u),v-u\rangle+\frac{\mu}{2}|v-u|^{2},

establishing
\mu
-strong convexity.
∎

We will also use the following definition of
L
-smoothness.

Definition 15.4
.

Function
{\mathsf{J}}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
is called
L
-smooth if for all
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
it holds that

|D{\mathsf{J}}(u)-D{\mathsf{J}}(v)|\leq L|u-v|.

∎

The following result provides a useful equivalent characterization of
L
-smoothness.

Theorem 15.5
.

Let
{\mathsf{J}}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
be convex and let
L>0
. The following are equivalent:

1.

{\mathsf{J}}
is
L
-smooth.

2.

For all
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
it holds that

(15.3)

{\mathsf{J}}(v)\leq{\mathsf{J}}(u)+\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u),v-u\rangle+\frac{L}{2}|v-u|^{2}.

Proof.

Suppose that
{\mathsf{J}}
is
L
-smooth.
As in the proof of Theorem
15.3
we have that for
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(15.4)

{\mathsf{J}}(v)-{\mathsf{J}}(u)=\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u),v-u\rangle+\int_{0}^{1}\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u+\theta(v-u))-D{\mathsf{J}}(u),v-u\rangle d\theta.

Applying the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and using
L
-smoothness gives

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(v)-{\mathsf{J}}(u)

\displaystyle\leq\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u),v-u\rangle+\int_{0}^{1}L\theta|v-u|^{2}d\theta

\displaystyle=\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u),v-u\rangle+\frac{L}{2}|v-u|^{2}.

Conversely, suppose that (
15.3
) holds. Fix

u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and define

\varphi(x):={\mathsf{J}}(x)-\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(v),x\rangle.

Since
{\mathsf{J}}
is convex and
D\varphi(v)=0
, point
v
is a minimizer of

\varphi
. Set

w:=u-\frac{1}{L}D\varphi(u).

Subtracting a linear function leaves the first-order Taylor remainder
unchanged, so (
15.3
) also holds for
\varphi
with
the same constant
L
.
Applying (
15.3
) to
\varphi
gives

\varphi(w)\leq\varphi(u)-\frac{1}{2L}|D\varphi(u)|^{2}.

Since
\varphi(v)\leq\varphi(w)
, it follows that

{\mathsf{J}}(u)-{\mathsf{J}}(v)-\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(v),u-v\rangle\geq\frac{1}{2L}|D{\mathsf{J}}(u)-D{\mathsf{J}}(v)|^{2}.

Swapping
u
and
v
and adding the resulting inequalities yields

\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u)-D{\mathsf{J}}(v),u-v\rangle\geq\frac{1}{L}|D{\mathsf{J}}(u)-D{\mathsf{J}}(v)|^{2}.

The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality now gives

|D{\mathsf{J}}(u)-D{\mathsf{J}}(v)|\leq L|u-v|,

establishing
L
-smoothness.
∎

Remark 15.6
.

Let
{\mathsf{J}}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
be
\mu
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth. We then claim that
\mu\leq L.

Indeed, let
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
u\neq v.
Then, using the characterization of
\mu
-strong convexity in
Theorem
15.3
, along with Cauchy–Schwarz and the definition of
L
-smoothness,
we deduce that

\displaystyle\mu|u-v|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u)-D{\mathsf{J}}(v),u-v\rangle

\displaystyle\leq|D{\mathsf{J}}(u)-D{\mathsf{J}}(v)||u-v|

\displaystyle\leq L|u-v|^{2}.

This shows that
\mu\leq L.
If
{\mathsf{J}}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
then
\mu
and
L
provide lower and upper bounds on the eigenvalues of the Hessian: it can be shown that
\mu I\preceq D^{2}{\mathsf{J}}(u)\preceq LI
for all
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

∎

We conclude this section with the definition of a convex set.

Definition 15.7
.

A set
S
in a vector space is
convex
if, for all
u,v\in S
and for all
\theta\in[0,1]
, we have that

\theta u+(1-\theta)v\in S.

∎

15.2
Gradient Descent

15.2.1
Deterministic Gradient Descent

Our starting point is the idea of a gradient flow:

Definition 15.8
.

Given function
{\mathsf{J}}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{+})
,

gradient flow
refers to the family of solutions
generated by the differential equation as the initial condition
u_{0}
is varied:

(15.5)

\frac{du}{dt}=-D{\mathsf{J}}(u),\quad u(0)=u_{0}.

∎

Theorem 15.9
.

Assume that
{\mathsf{J}}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{+})
has compact level sets.
Then a solution to (
15.5
) exists for all initial conditions
u_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and for all positive times
t.
Moreover,

(15.6)

\frac{d}{dt}{\mathsf{J}}\bigl(u(t))=-|D{\mathsf{J}}\bigl(u(t)\bigr)|^{2}.

Proof.

Since
{\mathsf{J}}
is
C^{2}
, vector field
D{\mathsf{J}}
is
C^{1}
and hence a local solution exists for every
initial condition
u_{0}.
Straightforward calculation reveals that,
for all
u_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and for all
t\geq 0
such that a solution exists, (
15.6
) holds.
Since
{\mathsf{J}}
is bounded from below, is
C^{2}
and has compact level sets, the fact that
{\mathsf{J}}(u(t))\leq{\mathsf{J}}(u_{0})

may be used to establish that
|u(t)|<\infty
for all
t\geq 0
and hence that, for all initial conditions
u_{0}
, a solution does indeed exist for all positive times
t
.
∎

Equation (
15.6
) implies that

{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)
decreases along trajectories, unless the trajectory is at a critical point
of
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot).
As a consequence, time-discretization
of equation (
15.5
) provides the basis of algorithms to minimize
{\mathsf{J}}.
This is motivation for the following:

Definition 15.10
.

Given function
{\mathsf{J}}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,

gradient descent
refers to
the iterative generation of sequence
\{u_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}

from a sequence of step-sizes
\{\alpha_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
by picking an
u_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and then iterating as follows:

(15.7)

u_{j+1}=u_{j}-\alpha_{j}D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j}),\qquad j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}.

∎

Remark 15.11
.

Note that equation (
15.7
) corresponds to Euler discretization of (
15.5
).
In the simplest case,
\alpha_{j}=\alpha
is some fixed constant time-step.
Often, however, the time-step sequence
\{\alpha_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
is determined
online as the algorithm progresses, with
\alpha_{j}
depending on
\{u_{i}\}_{i=0}^{j}.

In either case, knowing how to choose the time-step to ensure that
{\mathsf{J}}
decreases
at each step, unless it is at a critical point, requires knowledge of the properties of the vector field
D{\mathsf{J}}.

∎

The following theorem establishes linear convergence of gradient descent with a specific fixed step-size
\alpha
. We will assume that the objective
{\mathsf{J}}
is
L
-smooth and
\mu
-strongly convex. Recall that, as discussed in Remark
15.6
,
\mu\leq L.

Theorem 15.12
.

Suppose that
{\mathsf{J}}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
is
\mu
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth with constants
0<\mu\leq L
, and has minimizer
u^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Consider the gradient descent algorithm with step-size
\alpha_{j}\equiv\alpha:=1/L.
Then, at iteration
j\geq 1,

{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})\leq\Bigl(1-\frac{\mu}{L}\Bigr)^{j}\bigl({\mathsf{J}}(u_{0})-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})\bigr).

Proof.

First we show that
\mu
-strong convexity implies that, for all
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

(15.8)

|D{\mathsf{J}}(u)|^{2}\geq 2\mu\bigl({\mathsf{J}}(u)-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})\bigr).

Indeed, using
\mu
-strong convexity, we have

{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})\geq{\mathsf{J}}(u)+\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u),u^{\star}-u\rangle+\frac{\mu}{2}|u^{\star}-u|^{2}.

Thus, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we deduce that

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(u)-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})

\displaystyle\leq\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u),u-u^{\star}\rangle-\frac{\mu}{2}|u-u^{\star}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq|D{\mathsf{J}}(u)||u-u^{\star}|-\frac{\mu}{2}|u-u^{\star}|^{2}.

Let
\varphi(t):=|D{\mathsf{J}}(u)|t-\frac{\mu}{2}t^{2}.
The maximum of
\varphi
occurs at
t^{\star}=|D{\mathsf{J}}(u)|/\mu.
Moreover,
\varphi(t^{\star})=\frac{1}{2\mu}|D{\mathsf{J}}(u)|^{2}.
This implies (
15.8
).

Next, using the characterization of
L
-smoothness in Theorem
15.5
and that
u_{j+1}=u_{j}-\alpha D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})
with
\alpha=1/L
we have

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j+1})

\displaystyle\leq{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})-\alpha|D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})|^{2}+\frac{L\alpha^{2}}{2}|D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})|^{2}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})-\alpha\Bigl(1-\frac{L\alpha}{2}\Bigr)|D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})|^{2}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})-\frac{1}{2L}|D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})|^{2}.

By (
15.8
) we have that

|D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})|^{2}\geq 2\mu\bigl({\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})\bigr).

Therefore we conclude that

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j+1})-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})

\displaystyle\leq{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})-\frac{\mu}{L}\bigl({\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(1-\frac{\mu}{L}\Bigr)\bigl({\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})\bigr),

and the result follows by induction.
∎

Remark 15.13
.

Under the conditions of the previous theorem, it is also possible to show linear convergence of the optimization iterates. More precisely, it holds that
|u_{j}-u^{\star}|^{2}\leq(1-\mu/L)^{j}|u_{0}-u^{\star}|^{2}.

∎

Remark 15.14
.

For convex functions with Lipschitz gradients, there are modifications of gradient descent that have better convergence rates than the one stated above. For instance, Nesterov’s accelerated gradient descent achieves an objective error of order
j^{-2}
, which is optimal for this class of functions within an appropriate class of first-order methods. We will not discuss these other methods here but provide references in the bibliography.
∎

15.2.2
Stochastic Gradient Descent

Here we consider the minimization of an objective function
{\mathsf{J}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
that is defined by an expectation of a function
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. That is,

(15.9)

{\mathsf{J}}(u)=\int F(u,z)\,d\zeta(z),

where
\zeta
denotes the probability density function of the random variable
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}
. While explicit evaluations of
{\mathsf{J}}
or its gradients are challenging as a result of the expectation, we assume that it is possible to evaluate
D_{u}F(u,z)
for any realization of the random variable
z
.

Assuming that
\zeta
is replaced by an empirical approximation

\zeta^{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{z^{(n)}},

with the
z^{(n)}
sampled i.i.d. from
\zeta,
then
{\mathsf{J}}
is replaced by

(15.10)

{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(u)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}F(u,z^{(n)}).

The gradient of
{\mathsf{J}}^{N}
is then given by

(15.11)

D{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(u)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}D_{u}F(u,z^{(n)}).

This expression for
D{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(u)
has the interpretation as a Monte Carlo estimator of
the gradient
D{\mathsf{J}}(u)
defined by (
15.9
).

For large
N
, the evaluation of the sum (
15.11
) may be too expensive. Instead, one can pick
M<N
i.i.d. samples from
\zeta^{N}
; this corresponds to picking
M
random indices
\{\omega^{(m)}\}_{m=1}^{M}
, where the elements of this
set
\omega^{(m)}
are sampled uniformly with replacement from
1,\ldots,N
. Then the gradient can be approximated as

D{\mathsf{J}}^{N}(u)\approx\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}D_{u}F(u,z^{(\omega^{(m)})}).

This is known as a
mini-batch
. Carrying out gradient descent with (for example) an independent mini-batch is a widely used methodology for minimizing

{\mathsf{J}}^{N}
given by (
15.10
):

Definition 15.15
.

Given function
F\in C^{1,0}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}},\mathbb{R}),

stochastic gradient descent
for minimizing
{\mathsf{J}}^{N}
defined
by (
15.10
), refers to
the iterative generation of sequence
\{u_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}

from a sequence of step-sizes
\{\alpha_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}

and
j
-indexed sets
\{\omega_{j}^{(m)}\}_{m=1}^{M}
with points sampled uniformly with replacement from
1,\ldots,N
, independently for each
j
,
by picking any
u_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and then iterating as follows:

u_{j+1}=u_{j}-\frac{\alpha_{j}}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}D_{u}F(u_{j},z^{(\omega^{(m)}_{j})}).

∎

Remark 15.16
.

Even if the full gradient with respect to the empirical measure (
15.11
) can be feasibly computed, the stochasticity in stochastic gradient descent can be helpful in escaping from local minima for non-convex objective functions; in the context of optimizing neural networks
it is also sometimes advocated as a method to avoid overfitting.
∎

Although stochastic gradient descent is now widely used in the context of training neural networks, its history can be traced back to the 1950s where it was developed as a method to minimize intractable objective functions
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot)
formulated through an integral, as in (
15.9
). In this context we introduce the algorithm

(15.12)

u_{j+1}=u_{j}-\alpha_{j}g_{j},

where
g_{j}
is a random estimator of the gradient of
{\mathsf{J}}(\cdot).
For example we might take

g_{j}=D_{u}F(u_{j},z_{j}),\quad z_{j}\sim\zeta\,\,\text{i.i.d.}.

The following theorem shows that the stochastic gradient descent algorithm (
15.12
), with a constant step-size
\alpha
, can recover the desired minimum up to an error determined by the variance of the gradient estimates. This is followed by a second theorem which removes the error by using a decreasing step-size sequence
\alpha_{j}
.

Theorem 15.17
.

Suppose that
{\mathsf{J}}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
is defined by (
15.9
), is
\mu
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth for constants
0<\mu\leq L
, with minimizer
u^{\star}
. Consider the stochastic gradient descent algorithm (
15.12
)
with constant step-size
\alpha_{j}\equiv\alpha:=1/L.

Assume that
g_{j}
provides an unbiased estimate of the gradient with bounded variance:

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[g_{j}|u_{j}]=D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})

and
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[|g_{j}-D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})|^{2}|u_{j}]\leq\sigma^{2}.
Then

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})]\leq\Bigl(1-\frac{\mu}{L}\Bigr)^{j}\bigl({\mathsf{J}}(u_{0})-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})\bigr)+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2\mu}.

Proof.

As in the proof of Theorem
15.12
, it follows from
L
-smoothness that

{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j+1})\leq{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})-\frac{1}{L}\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j}),g_{j}\rangle+\frac{1}{2L}|g_{j}|^{2}.

Denote by
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{j}
expectation conditioned on
u_{j}.
Then,

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{j}{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j+1})\leq{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})-\frac{1}{L}|D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})|^{2}+\frac{1}{2L}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{j}|g_{j}|^{2}.

Now notice that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{j}|g_{j}|^{2}

\displaystyle=|D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})|^{2}+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{j}|g_{j}-D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq|D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})|^{2}+\sigma^{2}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{j}{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j+1})\leq{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})-\frac{1}{2L}|D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})|^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2L}.

Using inequality (
15.8
), we have

|D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})|^{2}\geq 2\mu\bigl({\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})\bigr).

Therefore,

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{j}[{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j+1})-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})]\leq\Bigl(1-\frac{\mu}{L}\Bigr)\bigl({\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})\Bigr)+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2L}.

Taking expectation over
u_{j}
and using induction yields the result.
∎

The next result shows convergence of stochastic gradient descent with variable step size.

Theorem 15.18
.

Suppose that
{\mathsf{J}}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
is
\mu
-strongly convex and

L
-smooth for constants
0<\mu\leq L
, with minimizer
u^{\star}
.
Assume that
g_{j}
is an unbiased estimator of the gradient with bounded
variance:

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[g_{j}|u_{j}]=D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j}),
and

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[|g_{j}-D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})|^{2}|u_{j}\bigr]\leq\sigma^{2}.

Consider the stochastic gradient descent algorithm (
15.12
) with
step-size

\alpha_{j}=\frac{2}{2L+\mu j}.

Then, for
\kappa:=\frac{2L}{\mu},

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})\bigr]\leq\frac{c}{j+\kappa}

for some constant
c
independent of
j
, but depending on
L,\mu,\sigma
and
u_{0}.

Proof.

Let
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{j}
denote expectation conditioned on
u_{j}
. By

L
-smoothness and Theorem
15.5
we have

{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j+1})\leq{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})+\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j}),u_{j+1}-u_{j}\rangle+\frac{L}{2}|u_{j+1}-u_{j}|^{2}.

Using the update
u_{j+1}=u_{j}-\alpha_{j}g_{j}
, the fact that
g_{j}
is an unbiased estimator of the gradient,
and subtracting
{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})
from both sides of the inequality, we obtain

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{j}{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j+1})-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})\leq{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})-\alpha_{j}|D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})|^{2}+\frac{L\alpha_{j}^{2}}{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{j}|g_{j}|^{2}.

The assumptions on
g_{j}
imply that

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{j}|g_{j}|^{2}\leq|D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})|^{2}+\sigma^{2}.

Since
\alpha_{j}\leq 1/L
, it follows that

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{j}{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j+1})-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})\leq{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})-\frac{\alpha_{j}}{2}|D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})|^{2}+\frac{L\alpha_{j}^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2}.

Let
e_{j}:=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})-{\mathsf{J}}(u^{\star})\bigr]
.
Using (
15.8
) and taking expectations gives
the recurrence

e_{j+1}\leq(1-\mu\alpha_{j})e_{j}+\frac{L\alpha_{j}^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2}.

Define
b:=\frac{2L\sigma^{2}}{\mu^{2}}.
Then

e_{j+1}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{2}{j+\kappa}}}\right)e_{j}+\frac{b}{(j+\kappa)^{2}}.

Note that, since
\mu\leq L
, the coefficient of
e_{j}
is non-negative. Choose
c\geq\max\{\kappa e_{0},b\}
. Then
e_{0}\leq c/\kappa
and the postulated bound holds for
j=0.

Using
b\leq c
straightforward induction shows that, for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,

e_{j}\leq\frac{c}{j+\kappa}\Rightarrow e_{j+1}\leq\frac{c}{j+1+\kappa}

The desired result follows.
∎

15.3
Newton and Gauss–Newton

15.3.1
Newton’s Method

Newton’s method is a common root-finding algorithm which, under suitable conditions and sufficiently close to a solution, benefits from quadratic convergence. This quadratic convergence improves on the
linear convergence of fixed point methods based on the contraction mapping principle. Newton’s method can also be applied to optimization, as we now show, leading to a methodology that improves on the linear convergence of gradient descent.

Definition 15.19
.

Given function
{\mathsf{J}}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})

with invertible Hessian,

Newton’s method
refers to
the iterative generation of sequence
\{u_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}

by picking any
u_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and then iterating as follows:

(15.13)

u_{j+1}=u_{j}-\bigl(D^{2}{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})\bigr)^{-1}D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j}).

∎

Note that critical points of
{\mathsf{J}}
are fixed points of the Newton iteration.
Newton’s method proceeds by optimizing successive quadratic approximations to
{\mathsf{J}}
, as the following theorem shows.

Theorem 15.20
.

Consider the Newton iteration (
15.13
). If
{\mathsf{J}}
is convex, then
u_{j+1}

is the unique minimizer of the quadratic approximation of
{\mathsf{J}}
around
u_{j}
.

Proof.

We first approximate
{\mathsf{J}}
around
u_{j}
by its second-order Taylor expansion:

(15.14)

{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j}+v)\approx{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})+\langle D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j}),v\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle v,D^{2}{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})v\rangle,

where
v
is any vector and the approximation is good when
v
is small. Since
{\mathsf{J}}
is convex, the Hessian is positive semidefinite, and the right-hand side of (
15.14
) is convex as a function of
v
, meaning that it can be minimized by setting the derivative to
0
. Differentiating the
approximation of
{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j}+v)
given in (
15.14
)
with respect to
v
, and then setting this derivative to
0
, gives

D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})+D^{2}{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})v=0,

and thus

v=-\bigl(D^{2}{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})\bigr)^{-1}D{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j}).

∎

15.3.2
Gauss–Newton

Let
\mathsf{G}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{m})
and let
\mathsf{G}=(\mathsf{G}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{G}_{m})
where

\mathsf{G}_{i}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}),

1\leq i\leq m
. Consider
the nonlinear least squares optimization problem

(15.15a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|\mathsf{G}(u)|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathsf{G}_{i}(u)^{2},

(15.15b)

\displaystyle u^{\star}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathsf{J}}(u).

Definition 15.21
.

Given function
\mathsf{G}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{m})

such that
\bigl(D\mathsf{G}(u)\bigr)^{\top}\bigl(D\mathsf{G}(u)\bigr)

is invertible for all
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
the
Gauss–Newton method
for minimizing nonlinear least squares problems of the form (
15.15
)
refers to the iterative generation of sequence

\{u_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}

by picking any
u_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and then iterating as follows:

\displaystyle u_{j+1}

\displaystyle=u_{j}-\Bigl(\bigl(D\mathsf{G}(u_{j})\bigr)^{\top}\bigl(D\mathsf{G}(u_{j})\bigr)\Bigr)^{-1}\bigl(D\mathsf{G}(u_{j})\bigr)^{\top}\mathsf{G}(u_{j}).

∎

Gauss–Newton results from applying Newton’s method (
15.13
) to (
15.15
a), and neglecting the second-order terms of
\mathsf{G}
in the Hessian of
{\mathsf{J}}
. To make this connection precise, suppose additionally that

\mathsf{G}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{m})
. Notice that

D{\mathsf{J}}(u)=\bigl(D\mathsf{G}(u)\bigr)^{\top}\mathsf{G}(u),

and that, entry-wise,

(15.16)

\bigl(D^{2}{\mathsf{J}}(u)\bigr)_{ab}=\bigl(D\mathsf{G}(u)^{\top}D\mathsf{G}(u)\bigr)_{ab}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathsf{G}_{i}(u)\bigl(D^{2}\mathsf{G}_{i}(u)\bigr)_{ab}.

Substituting these expressions into Newton’s method (
15.13
) and setting the second term in (
15.16
) to
0
, recovers the Gauss–Newton method. Making this approximation is computationally expedient as it avoids computation of the Hessians of the
\mathsf{G}_{k}.

It is also a good approximation near a minimizer
u^{\star}
of
{\mathsf{J}}

with small residual
\mathsf{G}(u^{\star})
, since the neglected term in
(
15.16
) is then small as the iterates approach
u^{\star}
.

15.4
Ensemble Kalman Inversion

This section discusses
Ensemble Kalman inversion
(EKI) as a general-purpose ensemble Kalman method for nonlinear least squares. Compared to the variants on gradient descent and Newton methods studied in the previous two sections, EKI has the advantage of being
derivative-free
. For this reason, EKI is widely used in inverse problems with complex forward models; indeed, as its name suggests, EKI was first developed as a method for numerical solution of inverse problems, and only more recently formulated as a general-purpose and broadly useful optimization algorithm.

Consider the cost function

{\mathsf{J}}(u)=\frac{1}{2}|y-G(u)|_{\Gamma}^{2},

for some (possibly) nonlinear function
G:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
, point
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and matrix
\Gamma\succ 0
. Notice that this nonlinear least squares objective is of the form in equation (
15.15
a); this can be seen by setting
m:=k
and
\mathsf{G}(u):=y-G(u).

Notice further that in the language of Chapter
1

the objective
{\mathsf{J}}(u)
is, up to an additive constant, the negative log-likelihood function determined by inverse problem (
1.1
) with Gaussian noise
\eta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Gamma);
that is, up to additive constant it holds that
{\mathsf{J}}(u)=-\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y|u)=-\log\mathsf{l}(y|u)
. Therefore, minimizing
{\mathsf{J}}(u)
corresponds to maximizing the likelihood, and this interpretation underpins the original derivation of the method.

EKI involves first drawing an initial set of
N
particles from a pre-specified probability measure; to be concrete
we will employ a Gaussian with a given mean
m_{0}
and covariance
C_{0}
. Thus

u_{0}^{(n)}\sim\mathcal{N}(m_{0},C_{0})\quad\text{i.i.d.},\quad n=1,\ldots,N.

EKI then evolves these particles for

j=0,\ldots,J-1
. At iteration
j
, we first compute

\displaystyle\overline{u}_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}u_{j}^{(n)},

\displaystyle\overline{G}_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}G(u_{j}^{(n)}),

\displaystyle C_{j}^{ug}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bigl(u_{j}^{(n)}-\overline{u}_{j}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(G(u_{j}^{(n)})-\overline{G}_{j}\bigr),

\displaystyle C_{j}^{gg}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bigl(G(u_{j}^{(n)})-\overline{G}_{j}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(G(u_{j}^{(n)})-\overline{G}_{j}\bigr),

\displaystyle K_{j}

\displaystyle=C_{j}^{ug}\bigl(C_{j}^{gg}+\Gamma\bigr)^{-1}.

The particles are then updated according to

\displaystyle u_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=u_{j}^{(n)}+K_{j}\bigl(y+\eta_{j+1}^{(n)}-G(u_{j}^{(n)})\bigr),\qquad n=1,\ldots,N,

\displaystyle\eta_{j+1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Gamma)\qquad\text{i.i.d.}

Remark 15.22
.

The algorithm produces an ensemble
\{u_{j}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
that, for each
j
, remains in the
linear span of the initial ensemble
\{u_{0}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}.
Thus the algorithm may be viewed
as seeking to minimize the objective function over a finite-dimensional subspace. In the case
of linear
G(\cdot)
the methodology is amenable to a complete theoretical analysis,
demonstrating sublinear convergence. However
variants on it, including Tikhonov regularization, restore linear convergence.
Continuous-time limits may also be derived,
and then analyzed analogously in the linear setting. It is
empirically observed that EKI often provides good approximations of the minimizer of the loss function in the nonlinear setting, and does so for relatively small
J
and
N
. See citations in the bibliography Section
15.8
.
∎

Remark 15.23
.

As the name suggests, there is a link between EKI and the
ensemble Kalman filter (EnKF) for data assimilation,
defined in Subsection
6.3.6
. One way of seeing this connection is via the formulation of certain maximum likelihood optimization problems as filtering problems. Another way of viewing the connection is
to note that, at every iteration, EKI approximates the solution to a nonlinear inverse problem using an ensemble approximation to the solution of a linear Gaussian inverse problem (Example
1.14
) and formally replacing the linear forward model with the nonlinear one. We also note that in the setting
of linear
G
the EKI methodology is connected to a preconditioned gradient descent. Finally we comment that ensemble approximations of other optimization methods, such as Gauss–Newton, are also in use, popular because they avoid the need to compute derivatives.
These connections are detailed in the bibliography Section
15.8
.
∎

15.5
Expectation Maximization

The expectation maximization (EM)
algorithm is a general-purpose approach to maximum likelihood estimation with latent variables.
Let
u\in\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
be the parameter of interest,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
the data, and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}
a latent variable. We seek to compute the maximum likelihood estimator of parameter
u
given data
y;
that is, we seek to maximize

(15.17)

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y|u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,z|u)\,dz.

The EM algorithm is useful when we cannot readily evaluate the likelihood
{\mathsf{J}}(u)=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y|u)
or compute its gradient, but evaluating the
full likelihood

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,z|u)
is possible. Variable
z
is sometimes referred to as
missing data
.

Example 15.24
.

Recall the stochastic dynamics and observation models
summarized in equations (
8.1
):

\displaystyle v^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\vartheta}(v^{\dagger}_{j})+\xi^{\dagger}_{j},

\displaystyle\xi^{\dagger}_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)\text{ i.i.d.},

\displaystyle y^{\dagger}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=h(v^{\dagger}_{j+1})+\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1},

\displaystyle\eta^{\dagger}_{j+1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Gamma)\text{ i.i.d.}

Recall that we refer to
\{v^{\dagger}_{j}\}
as the signal and
\{y^{\dagger}_{j}\}

as the observation sequence. Here
v^{\dagger}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(m_{0},C_{0})

and we assume the independence structure summarized in Assumption
6.1
. Take
u=\vartheta
,

z=\{v^{\dagger}_{0},\ldots,v^{\dagger}_{J}\}
and
y=\{y^{\dagger}_{1},\ldots,y^{\dagger}_{J}\}.
Consider the problem of
computing the maximum likelihood estimator for
u
, given observations
y
; we assume that
z
is not known to us.
This has precisely the structure
(
15.17
); in particular
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,z|u)
can be written down in closed form, but in general

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y|u)
cannot be. To solve the maximum likelihood estimation problem it is necessary to obtain information about the missing data

z
– here, the signal. See Section
8.1
for application of the ideas in this section
to this specific parameter estimation problem for
\vartheta.

∎

The objective function in (
15.17
) for the EM algorithm resembles that in (
15.9
) for stochastic gradient descent, where intractable objective
{\mathsf{J}}(u)
is defined by averaging tractable objective
F(u,z)
over auxiliary variables
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}.

Notice, however, that in this section we depart from the convention in previous sections and formulate the optimization problem in terms of
maximizing
rather than
minimizing
objective
{\mathsf{J}}.

A key component in the derivation of the EM algorithm is encapsulated in the following definition:

Definition 15.25
.

The
evidence lower bound
(
\mathsf{ELBO}
) of a probability density
q
in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}
with respect to an unnormalized density
\widetilde{\pi}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}
is given by

\mathsf{ELBO}(\widetilde{\pi},q)=\mathbb{E}^{q}[\log\widetilde{\pi}]-\mathbb{E}^{q}[\log q].

∎

Remark 15.26
.

Recall the KL divergence introduced in Definition
11.39
.
Note that if
\pi
is a normalized probability density then

\mathsf{ELBO}(\pi,q)=-\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}(q\|\pi).

∎

It is useful to define

(15.18a)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{J}}(u):=

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y|u),

(15.18b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(q,u):=

\displaystyle\mathsf{ELBO}\bigl(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,\cdot\,|u),q\bigr).

Note that
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,z|u)
is an unnormalized density for variable
z
alone, with
(y,u)
fixed; however, it is normalized with respect to the joint distribution on the pair
(y,z)
.
The following result connects the joint distribution
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,z|u)
to
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y|u)

and is therefore useful for maximum likelihood estimation, in the context of the identity (
15.17
).

Theorem 15.27
.

Let
q
be a probability density function over latent variable
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}.

It holds that

(15.19)

\log{\mathsf{J}}(u)=\mathcal{L}(q,u)+\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(q\|\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot\,|y,u)\bigr).

Proof.

Using the definition of
\mathcal{L}(q,u),
product rule, and the Definition
11.39
of the KL divergence, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(q,u)

\displaystyle=\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}}\log\biggl(\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,z|u)}{q(z)}\biggr)q(z)\,dz

\displaystyle=\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}}\log\biggl(\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(z|y,u)}{q(z)}\biggr)q(z)\,dz+\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}}\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y|u)q(z)\,dz

\displaystyle=-\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(q\|\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot\,|y,u)\bigr)+\log{\mathsf{J}}(u),

as desired.
∎

Note that the left-hand side of identity (
15.19
) is independent of density
q
. Noting that
q

may be chosen freely, and since all divergences are non-negative, the following is immediate:

Corollary 15.28
.

For all
q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}})
and all
u\in\Theta
it holds that
\log{\mathsf{J}}(u)\geq\mathcal{L}(q,u).

Recall that we are interested in maximizing
{\mathsf{J}}(u)
, and hence
\log{\mathsf{J}}(u)
, over
u
. Recall also that we have no direct
access to the missing data
z
. Corollary (
15.28
) suggests that we may instead try to maximize
\mathcal{L}(q,u).

The EM algorithm is an iterative method which generates sequence
\{u_{j},q_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
by alternating the maximization of
\mathcal{L}(q,u)
with respect to
q
and
u.
The density

q
is chosen in a manner which builds into the algorithm learned information about
dependence on the missing data
z
. The high-level structure of the algorithm is:

1.

Set
q_{j}\in\argmax_{q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}})}\mathcal{L}(q,u_{j}).

2.

Set
u_{j+1}\in\argmax_{u\in\Theta}\mathcal{L}(q_{j},u)
.

Step 1 yields
q_{j}(z)=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(z|y,u_{j})
; this is because
(
15.19
) shows that the maximization of
\mathcal{L}(q,u_{j})
over
q

is the same as minimization of
\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(q\|\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot\,|y,u_{j})\bigr)
over
q
. Density

q_{j}(z)=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(z|y,u_{j})
achieves the global minimum of this non-negative function and
we have that

(15.20)

\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(q_{j}\|\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot\,|y,u_{j})\bigr)=0.

We thus obtain the identity

(15.21)

\log{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})=\mathcal{L}(q_{j},u_{j}).

Step 2 proceeds by noting that

(15.22a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(q_{j},u)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ELBO}\bigl(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,\cdot\,|u),q_{j}\bigr),

(15.22b)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{q_{j}}[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,\cdot\,|u)]-\mathbb{E}^{q_{j}}[\log q_{j}],

(15.22c)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}}\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,z|u)\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(z|y,u_{j})\,dz+\mathsf{const}.

Here
\mathsf{const}
is independent of
u
, but does depend on
u_{j}
; since, in this step, maximization is over
u
, it is irrelevant.
Hence, the quantity to maximize is the expected value of the joint log-density
\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,z|u)
with respect to
q_{j}(z)=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(z|y,u_{j});
we see explicitly that
q_{j}
thus
represents the distribution
of the missing data
z
, given the data
y
and current parameter estimate
u_{j}.

In summary, the E-step corresponds to computing
\mathcal{L}(q_{j},u)
from (
15.22
); the M-step
corresponds to maximizing the resulting expression over
u
to obtain
u_{j+1}.

Combining these two steps gives the EM algorithm:

Algorithm 5

Expectation Maximization

1:

Input
: Initialization
u_{0}.

2:

For
j=0,1,\ldots
do the following expectation and maximization steps:

3:

E-Step
: Compute

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{z\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(z|y,u_{j})}\Bigl[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,z|u)\Bigr]=\int\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,z|u)\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(z|y,u_{j})\,dz.

4:

M-Step
: Compute

u_{j+1}\in\argmax_{u\in\Theta}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{z\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(z|y,u_{j})}\Bigl[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,z|u)\Bigr].

5:

Output
: Parameter estimates
\{u_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}.

Example 15.29
.

We return to hidden Markov model Example
15.24
.
The E-step requires the smoothing distribution
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(z|y,u_{j})
,
at current parameter estimation step. The M-step requires formula for the joint distribution of
signal and observation, at arbitrary parameter
u
; and maximization of this function when averaged
with respect to the smoothing distribution. For practical reasons, the smoothing distribution is sometimes
replaced by the filtering distribution as this may be easier to approximate.
∎

The following result shows that the EM framework has the desirable property that the likelihood function increases monotonically along iterates
u_{j}.

Theorem 15.30
.

Let
\{u_{j}\}_{j\geq 0}
be the iterates of the EM Algorithm
5
. Then,

{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})\leq{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j+1}).

Proof.

Since the logarithm is an increasing function in its domain, it suffices to show that
\log{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j})\leq\log{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j+1}).

Let
q_{j}(z)=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(z|y,u_{j}).
Using the log-likelihood characterization in Theorem
15.27
, it holds that

\displaystyle\log{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j+1})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}(q_{j},u_{j+1})+\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(q_{j}\|\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot\,|y,u_{j+1})\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{L}(q_{j},u_{j})+\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(q_{j}\|\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot\,|y,u_{j+1})\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{L}(q_{j},u_{j})+\mathsf{D}_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm KL}}}\bigl(q_{j}\|\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot\,|y,u_{j})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\log{\mathsf{J}}(u_{j}).

The first inequality follows because
u_{j+1}\in\argmax_{u}\mathcal{L}(q_{j},u);
the second follows from (
15.20
) and the fact that all divergences are non-negative; the final equality follows from (
15.20
) and (
15.21
).
∎

Remark 15.31
.

The monotonic increase of the likelihood does not guarantee, in general, that the EM parameter estimates
\{u_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
converge in the large
j
limit.
Convergence of parameter estimates to a local maximizer of the likelihood function can be established
under additional assumptions.
It is important to note, however, that the expectation in the E-step and the optimization in the M-step are often intractable. Monte Carlo, filtering, or smoothing algorithms may be employed to approximate the E-step, and optimization algorithms to approximate the M-step. Such approximations can cause loss of monotonicity and loss of convergence guarantees.
∎

We conclude with an independent and direct proof of Corollary
15.28
.

Lemma 15.32
.

Let
q
be a probability density function in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}
and let
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,z|u),
viewed as a function of
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}
with
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
fixed, be the unnormalized density of latent variable
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}.
It holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{ELBO}\bigl(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,\cdot\,|u),q\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y|u),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(q,u)

\displaystyle\leq\log{\mathsf{J}}(u).

Proof.

This lower bound follows by using the Jensen inequality:

\displaystyle\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y|u)

\displaystyle=\log\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,z|u)\,dz

\displaystyle=\log\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}}\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,z|u)}{q(z)}q(z)\,dz

\displaystyle\geq\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}}\log\biggl(\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,z|u)}{q(z)}\biggr)q(z)\,dz

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ELBO}(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(y,\cdot\,|u),q).

The second inequality in the statement of the lemma follows directly from the definitions.
∎

15.6
Markov Chain Monte Carlo

In this section we turn our attention to the problem of drawing samples from a given target distribution
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).
We will focus on Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) algorithms, a wide class of methods that share the idea of generating samples from a Markov chain for which the target is an invariant distribution.
To describe MCMC we first recall that a
Markov kernel
is a function
p:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying:

•

p(u,v)\geq 0
for all
(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d};
and

•

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p(u,v)\,dv=1
for all
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

For fixed
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

p(u,\cdot)
defines a probability density function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}.

From this kernel we can define a Markov chain through the iteration

(15.24)

u^{(n+1)}\sim p(u^{(n)},\cdot),\qquad 0\leq n\leq N-1.

Note that if the chain is currently at
u^{(n)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
then
p(u^{(n)},\cdot)
defines the probability with which the chain will be in any given Borel subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
after one time step.
The idea of MCMC is to define a Markov kernel
with the property that the target
\pi
is an invariant distribution for the kernel.
This means that if we sample an initial draw
u^{(0)}\sim\pi

then it holds that
u^{(n)}\sim\pi
for all
1\leq n\leq N.

It is possible to show, under mild assumptions, that even when the initial draw
u^{(0)}
is sampled from initial distribution
\pi_{0}
different from
\pi
, the distribution of the
n
-th draw
u^{(n)}
defined by (
15.24
) converges to
\pi
in the large
n
asymptotic. Ergodicity
(see Section
14.1
) then enables approximate computation of expectations
under
\pi
from a single long trajectory.
In the next two subsections we introduce two classes of MCMC methods that are widely used in inverse problems, data assimilation and other applications: the Metropolis–Hastings algorithm and the Gibbs sampler.

15.6.1
The Metropolis–Hastings Algorithm

We consider first the Metropolis–Hastings algorithm, a versatile MCMC approach to define a Markov kernel
p_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MH}}}
that can be readily sampled and which satisfies
detailed balance
with respect to
\pi;
that is, for all
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
it holds that

(15.25)

\pi(u)p_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MH}}}(u,v)=\pi(v)p_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MH}}}(v,u);

this means that the probability of being in state
u
and transitioning from
u
to
v
is equal to the probability of being in state
v
and transitioning from
v
to
u
.
Integrating over
u
delivers

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\pi(u)p_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MH}}}(u,v)du=\pi(v)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MH}}}(v,u)du=\pi(v),

which is the desired invariance property.
The key idea behind the Metropolis–Hastings algorithm is that kernel
p_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MH}}}
satisfying (
15.25
) can be defined by sampling from a proposal Markov kernel
q,
chosen to be easy to sample from, and an accept/reject step. Given
the
n
-th sample
u^{(n)},
we obtain the
(n+1)
-th sample through a two-step process. First, we propose
v^{*}\sim q(u^{(n)},\cdot)
by sampling from the
proposal
Markov kernel. Second, we accept this proposal and set
u^{(n+1)}=v^{*}
with probability
a(u^{(n)},v^{*});
otherwise, we reject it and set

u^{(n+1)}=u^{(n)}.
The Metropolis–Hastings acceptance probability is defined by

(15.26)

a(u,v):=\min\biggl\{1,\frac{\pi(v)}{\pi(u)}\frac{q(v,u)}{q(u,v)}\biggr\},\qquad u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

This definition ensures that the Markov kernel
p_{\mbox{\tiny{\rm MH}}}
that determines the probabilistic transition from
u^{(n)}
to
u^{(n+1)}
satisfies the detailed balance condition (
15.25
). Algorithm
6
outlines the procedure.

Algorithm 6

Metropolis–Hastings Algorithm

1:

Input
: Target distribution
\pi
, initial distribution
\pi_{0},
proposal Markov kernel
q
, test function
f,
number of samples
N.

2:

Initial Draw
: Draw initial sample
u^{(0)}\sim\pi_{0}.

3:

Subsequent Samples
: For
n=0,1,\dots,N-1
do:

1.

Sample
v^{\star}\sim q(u^{(n)},\cdot).

2.

Update

\displaystyle u^{(n+1)}=\begin{cases}v^{\star},&\text{with probability}\,\,a(u^{(n)},v^{*})\,\,\text{using}\,\,\eqref{eq:mhaccept}.\\
u^{(n)},&\text{with probability}\,\,1-a(u^{(n)},v^{*}).\end{cases}

4:

Output
: Samples
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
and approximation
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}^{u\sim\pi}[f(u)]\approx\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}f(u^{(n)}).

Remark 15.33
.

Notice that the target distribution only enters the Metropolis–Hastings algorithm in the acceptance probability (
15.26
); since this formula only involves the
ratio
of the target density at the proposed and current values of the chain, the acceptance probability can be computed even when the target distribution is only known up to normalization constant.
∎

Algorithm

Proposal

Random Walk Metropolis–Hastings

q(u,\cdot)=\mathcal{N}(u,\beta I_{d})

Metropolis-Adjusted Langevin

q(u,\cdot)=\mathcal{N}(u+\frac{\beta}{2}D\log\pi(u),\beta I_{d})

Preconditioned Crank-Nicolson

q(u,\cdot)=\mathcal{N}\bigl((1-\beta)^{1/2}u,\beta I_{d}\bigr)

Table 15.1:
